# Sūrah al-Naml (Chapter 27) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Named After the Smallest Member of Its Cast**

Al-Naml, "The Ants," is the twenty-seventh chapter of the Qur'an: ninety-three verses, revealed in Makkah, placed by the canonical order between al-Shuʿarāʾ (26) and al-Qaṣaṣ (28). Its name comes from a single moment in verse 18 — the passing of Solomon's immense army through a valley where an ant addresses her community. It is one of the most striking choices of a chapter-title in the whole Book. Other sūrahs are named for a cow, a bee, a spider, a table, iron, or the dawn; but here the title is taken from the smallest, least noticed creature in a narrative that also contains a prophet-king, an army of jinn and men and birds, and a queen ruling one of the wealthiest kingdoms of Arabia. The Qur'an names the chapter after the ant. That single editorial fact is a lesson before the first verse is read: in the divine accounting, significance is not measured by size, rank, or volume of voice, and the least of creatures may speak the sentence that a prophet stops an army to hear.

The name also fixes the sūrah's central subject, which is knowledge — knowledge in every register: the knowledge given to David and Solomon (v. 15), the "language of the birds" (v. 16), the ant's practical knowledge of danger (v. 18), the hoopoe's knowledge of a distant land (v. 22), the "knowledge of the Book" that transports a throne in a glance (v. 40), the queen's careful judgment (v. 42), God's knowledge of what breasts conceal (v. 74), the Preserved Tablet in which nothing hidden is absent (v. 75), and the final announcement that God will show people His signs until they know them (v. 93). Between these poles — the knowledge of an ant and the knowledge of God — the sūrah arranges the whole drama of faith: what human beings can know, what they cannot, and what they must do with the little they have.

**A Makkan Sūrah with a Political Imagination**

Most Makkan revelation addresses a persecuted minority and concentrates on God, the soul, the resurrection, and the fate of earlier peoples. Al-Naml does all of that, and then does something unusual: it devotes nearly half its length to prophets who governed. Solomon commands an army, administers discipline, receives envoys, writes diplomatic correspondence, evaluates reports, tests witnesses, and presides over a court of notables. David is mentioned beside him as a recipient of knowledge. The sūrah therefore answers a question that the early Muslims — poor, hunted, and politically irrelevant in Makkah — must have asked constantly: what does faith look like when it holds power? The answer given is not that power is evil or that it is the reward of piety, but that power is a trust whose test is gratitude. Solomon's response to the arrival of a throne across a continent is not triumph but supplication: *"This is of the Bounty of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful"* (v. 40). The sūrah's model of governance is the model of a man who hears an ant.

Set against this are the sūrah's other two case-studies in community: Thamūd, where nine men who "worked corruption in the land and did not set matters aright" (v. 48) plot a night assassination of their prophet; and the people of Lot, whose reply to moral counsel is to propose expelling the only pure family in their town (v. 56). Power without gratitude becomes conspiracy; desire without discipline becomes persecution of the pure. Between them stands Bilqīs, the Queen of Sheba, who is given a throne, a council, an army, and a false religion — and who, unlike Pharaoh in the parallel narratives, surrenders her throne and her god together. Her story occupies more verses than any other in the chapter, and it is the Qur'an's fullest portrait of a female ruler exercising consultation, restraint, and finally honest judgment.

**The Three Ṭā-Sīn Chapters and Their Shared Architecture**

Al-Naml is the middle of a triplet. Sūrah 26 (al-Shuʿarāʾ), sūrah 27 (al-Naml), and sūrah 28 (al-Qaṣaṣ) all open with the disconnected letters *Ṭā Sīn*, and all three run the same narrative cycle: Moses at the fire, then Solomon or the earlier peoples, then the closing confrontation between the Qur'an and those who reject it. Reading them in sequence makes the design of the Qur'an's own arrangement visible. Al-Shuʿarāʾ is the sūrah of the messengers rejected — it repeats six times, almost liturgically, *"And indeed thy Lord, He is the Mighty, the Merciful"*; al-Qaṣaṣ is the sūrah of the story told continuously, following Moses from birth to exile to return; al-Naml stands between them as the sūrah of *evidence* — of what prophets are given and how they are to be answered. Its refrain is not a divine name but a question, and that question is repeated five times in five verses: *"Is there a god alongside God?"* (a-ilāhun maʿa Llāh, vv. 60, 61, 62, 63, 64). No other sūrah of the Qur'an repeats a sentence so insistently within so few verses. The effect is that of a cross-examination: each of the five questions builds on a different observable fact — rain and gardens, rivers and mountains, the answered prayer of the desperate, guidance in darkness, and creation begun and returned — and each ends by leaving the hearer with nowhere to stand except an answer.

**The Refrain That Ends Every Argument**

The rhetorical method of verses 59–64 deserves notice before the commentary reaches them, because it is one of the Qur'an's most characteristic modes of proof. The verses begin with *amman khalaqa* — "Is He [not better] who created..." — a construction that promises a comparison and then withdraws it. Grammatically the sentence opens as though a second party will be named for comparison; instead it arrives at the question *"Is there a god alongside God?"* The commentators observe that the comparison is left unspoken deliberately: God's creative act is so manifest that naming a rival beside it would dignify an absurdity. The logic is the logic of the courtroom where counsel asks, "Is there any other person in this room who did this?" and the silence is the verdict. Then each of the five verses closes with a diagnosis of the hearer rather than an insult: *they ascribe equals*, *most of them know not*, *little do you reflect*, *exalted is God above what they associate*, *bring your proof if you are truthful*. The sequence moves from accusation to invitation: the last of the five asks for evidence. A text that ends its theology with "bring your proof" is a text confident in its own ground.

**The Ant, the Hoopoe, and the Discipline of Attention**

Two ḥadīths from the Prophetic corpus belong to this sūrah's world and help fix its moral temperature. The first is recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayrah, and the classical commentators cite it precisely at verses 18–19:

> *"An ant bit one of the prophets, so he ordered the ants' nest to be burnt, and it was burnt. Then God revealed to him: 'Because one ant bit you, did you destroy a community (ummah) among the communities that glorify [God]? Why not a single ant?'"* (al-Bukhārī 3019; Muslim 2241)

The word *ummah* — the same word used for a religious community of human beings — is applied here to ants. The rebuke is a lesson in proportionality, in the value of a community, and in the danger of retaliating collectively for an individual injury. The second ḥadīth, recorded by Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah, and Aḥmad from Ibn ʿAbbās, extends the same protection by name to the very creatures of this sūrah:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ forbade the killing of four kinds of animal: the ant, the bee, the hoopoe, and the shrike."* (Abū Dāwūd 5267; Ibn Mājah 3224; Aḥmad)

The ant of verse 18 and the hoopoe (*hudhud*) of verses 20–22 are therefore not merely literary decorations in the Muslim imagination; they are named in Prophetic law as creatures whose lives are protected. A sūrah that gives an ant a speaking part and a hoopoe a diplomatic mission produces, in the lived tradition, a rule against killing either. That is what revelation does: it converts a story into a conscience.

**Solomon and the ʿIfrīt**

The third ḥadīth belongs to verses 38–40, where an *ʿifrīt* of the jinn volunteers to carry the queen's throne. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record from Abū Hurayrah and from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī that the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"An ʿifrīt of the jinn sought to attack me suddenly last night to break off my prayer, but God enabled me to overpower him... and I intended to tie him to one of the pillars of the mosque so that you might all look at him in the morning. Then I remembered the supplication of my brother Solomon: 'My Lord, forgive me and grant me a kingdom that belongs to no one after me' — so I let him go, humiliated."* (al-Bukhārī 461; Muslim 541; the wording varies between the collections)

The report matters here for two reasons. It confirms that the Qur'anic account of Solomon's authority over the jinn was understood by the Prophet ﷺ himself as a real, historical authority and not as metaphor; and it shows the Prophet ﷺ declining to use a power that was specifically given to another prophet, honoring the boundary Solomon had asked God to draw. The sūrah's portrait of power is thus completed: Solomon has an army no human being has ever had, and he spends his life saying thank you.

**Solomon's Supplication Made the Muslims' Daily Duʿāʾ**

The prayer of verse 19 — *"My Lord! Inspire me to give thanks for Thy blessing wherewith Thou hast blessed me and my parents"* — did not remain inside the story. The Prophet ﷺ took it up and taught it to his Companions as a daily practice. Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī record from Muʿādh ibn Jabal:

> *"The Messenger of God ﷺ took my hand and said: 'O Muʿādh, by God, I love you.' Then he said: 'I advise you, O Muʿādh, never to fail to say at the end of every prayer: Allāhumma aʿinnī ʿalā dhikrika wa shukrika wa ḥusni ʿibādatika — O God, help me to remember You, to thank You, and to worship You well.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 1522; al-Nasāʾī 1303; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The structure of that duʿāʾ — remembrance, gratitude, and good worship — is the structure of verse 19, whose words are *awziʿnī an ashkura niʿmatak* ("inspire me to give thanks for Thy blessing"). Every Muslim who has ever ended a prayer with those words has been praying in Solomon's grammar. The sūrah is thereby connected to the reader's own tongue, which is how the Qur'an always prefers to work: it does not leave its stories in the past.

**The End of the World and the Beast**

The last third of al-Naml shifts from history to eschatology, and it is here that the sūrah contains one of the most discussed verses of the entire Qur'an: verse 82, on the *dābbah* — the beast that will come forth from the earth and speak to human beings. It is one of the ten great signs of the Hour in the Islamic tradition, and Muslim records the Prophetic statement that binds it to the closing of the door of repentance:

> *"There are three things which, when they appear, no soul's faith will benefit it if it did not believe before, or did not earn good through its faith: the rising of the sun from its place of setting, the Dajjāl, and the beast of the earth."* (Muslim 158, from Abū Hurayrah)

The commentators differ extensively about the beast's form, size, place of emergence, and words, and al-Rāzī's methodological caution — that nothing should be asserted about such matters except what can be authenticated from the Prophet ﷺ — will be followed in the commentary below. The verse's own point is not zoological but moral: it describes a moment when certainty arrives too late to be useful, because it arrives without choice.

**The Final Verses: A Sūrah That Closes on the Qur'an Itself**

The sūrah ends where it began. Verse 1 announced *"These are the signs of the Qur'an and a clear Book"*; verse 76 announces that *"this Qur'an recounts unto the Children of Israel most of that wherein they differ"*; verse 91 records the Prophet ﷺ commanded *"to worship the Lord of this city, Who has made it sacred"* and *"to recite the Qur'an"*; and verse 93 closes with praise, a promise of signs, and the reminder that *"thy Lord is not heedless of what you do."* The movement is complete: a Book is named, a history is retold, a sanctuary is identified, a recitation is commanded, and a warning is left standing. Between the opening and the closing, the sūrah has walked its reader through a valley of ants, a court of jinn, a queen's throne, a murdered camel, a burnt city, five unanswered questions, a trumpet, and mountains that drift like cloud — all of it in the service of one sentence that appears five times and is never answered by anyone in the chapter, because no answer exists.

What follows is an expanded, verse-by-verse commentary on all ninety-three verses in strict order. Each verse is treated as an independent and complete discourse: quoted on its own, then developed through the Arabic language and its roots, through cross-references to other Qur'anic verses given in their actual wording, through authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, through the positions of the classical commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Bayḍāwī, Ibn ʿArabī, Ibn ʿAjībah, and others — through questions of creed, divine justice, and human responsibility, and through the practical and psychological implications for a reader living today. Verses grouped together in the source commentary (for example 10–11, 13–14, 18–19, 22–23, 27–28, 29–30, 32–33, 49–50, 54–55, 59–64, 80–81, 89–90) are separated here and given full individual treatment, as the verse-by-verse method requires. Nothing has been compressed because the chapter is long.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:1

> **Ṭā. Sīn. These are the signs of the Quran and a clear Book,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Letters Standing at the Door of Ninety-Three Verses**

The twenty-seventh chapter of the Qur'an begins, as the twenty-sixth and twenty-eighth do, with two isolated letters pronounced by their names: *Ṭā. Sīn.* They are not words; they carry no lexical meaning in Arabic; they are not the initials of a title. Twenty-nine sūrahs of the Qur'an open with such letters — one (*Qāf*, *Nūn*, *Ṣād*), two (*Ṭā Sīn*, *Ṭā Hā*, *Ḥā Mīm*, *Yā Sīn*), three (*Alif Lām Mīm*, *Alif Lām Rā*), four (*Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād*), or five (*Kāf Hā Yā ʿAyn Ṣād*, *Ḥā Mīm ʿAyn Sīn Qāf*) — and from the earliest generation they have been the object of wonder, argument, and reverent silence. Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq is reported to have said:

> *"Every book has a mystery (sirr), and the mystery of the Qur'an is in the openings of the sūrahs."*

The letters of *Ṭā Sīn* specifically have attracted a range of explanations recorded by al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī, and al-Qurṭubī. Some said the *ṭāʾ* stands for the Divine Name *al-Laṭīf*, "the Subtle," and the *sīn* for *al-Samīʿ*, "the Hearing" — a reading that treats the letters as an abbreviation of names by which God is invoked. Others said the *ṭāʾ* indicates *ṭahārah*, purity, and the *sīn* the *sirr*, the secret, of the Beloved. Both readings belong to a devotional rather than a linguistic register: they are not claims about etymology but meditations offered by readers who found the letters fruitful.

**The Argument in the Alphabet Itself**

The most widely held classical explanation is different in kind and stronger in force. Ibn ʿAbbās, and after him al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, and Ibn Kathīr, treated the disconnected letters as a demonstration of the Qur'an's inimitability (*iʿjāz*). The reasoning is simple and hard to escape. The Arabs of the seventh century were a people whose supreme cultural achievement was language: their poets composed odes that were memorized for generations, and their markets held poetry competitions whose winners' verses were hung in cloth of gold. The Qur'an was revealed to them in their own tongue, and its challenge to them was explicit:

> *"And if you are in doubt concerning what We sent down upon Our servant, then bring a sūrah like it, and call upon your witnesses apart from God, if you are truthful."* (Qur'an 2:23)

> *"Say: If mankind and the jinn gathered together to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce the like of it, even if they backed one another."* (Qur'an 17:88)

Now consider what the disconnected letters do in that light. The sūrah opens by displaying the raw material — the letters themselves, *ṭāʾ* and *sīn*, two of the twenty-eight signs from which every Arabic word is built. It is as though a master craftsman set his tools on the table before his rivals and said: this is the entire inventory; here are the letters you use every day; from these — and from nothing else — this Book is made. The challenge is not only stated but *performed*, in the very medium of the challenged. Al-Rāzī develops the point with care: if the Qur'an were composed of foreign words or of a language unknown to its hearers, its inimitability would prove nothing; its miracle lies precisely in being built from the same alphabet as the poems the Arabs considered unsurpassable.

A second observation reinforces the first. In nearly every sūrah that begins with these letters, the letters are followed immediately by mention of the Book, revelation, or the Qur'an itself. Here: *Ṭā. Sīn. These are the signs of the Qur'an.* At 2:1–2: *Alif Lām Mīm. That is the Book.* At 10:1: *Alif Lām Rā. These are the signs of the wise Book.* At 26:1–2: *Ṭā Sīn Mīm. These are the signs of the clear Book.* At 28:1–2: *Ṭā Sīn Mīm. These are the signs of the clear Book.* The pattern is consistent enough that the commentators took it as a rule: the letters are a herald, and their function is to arrest attention and announce that what follows is speech of an unusual order. The listener, expecting a story or a command, receives instead the alphabet — and is thereby made to notice that he is listening.

**"Those Are the Signs": A Reference to Something Already Heard**

The second half of the verse deserves close attention, because its grammar is doing real work. *Tilka āyātu'l-Qurʾān* — "**Those** are the signs of the Qur'an." The demonstrative is *tilka*, the feminine plural form of *dhālika*, "that/those." Arabic uses the distant demonstrative, rather than the near one (*hādhihi*, "these"), for a specific effect noted by al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī: it points to something of high rank and established reality, and here, more concretely, to verses the audience had *already heard*. At the time of this sūrah's revelation in Makkah, a substantial portion of the Qur'an had been recited publicly over more than a decade — at the Kaʿbah, in the houses of the Companions, in the recitation of prayer, in the confrontation at Badr's eve. The Qur'an had become, in a real sense, a known quantity, something out there in the world, already recited and already heard. So the verse says: *those* — the ones you have been hearing, the ones already in your ears and your memory — *those* are the signs of the Qur'an.

The word *āyāt* is itself double. Its root *ʾ-y-y* gives a sign, a mark, a proof, a wonder, and — in Qur'anic usage — a verse. The Qur'an uses the same word for the proofs of God in nature and for its own sentences, and the overlap is deliberate: each verse of the Book is meant to function as a sign in the same way that rain, mountains, and the alternation of night and day function as signs. A sign is something that points beyond itself. To call the Qur'an's units *āyāt* is to say that they are not ends but pointers — and the sūrah will spend its last section (vv. 59–64) doing exactly that, turning five observed facts of the world into five pointers toward a single conclusion.

**"A Clear Book": The Word That Makes Things Clear**

The final phrase is *wa kitābin mubīn* — "and a Book that is *mubīn*." The word comes from the root *b-y-n*, meaning separation, distinction, and consequently clarity: to make a thing *bayyin* is to separate it from what it could be confused with, so that it stands out and is recognized. The active participle *mubīn* carries both senses that Arabic participles of this form carry — it is *clear in itself* and it *makes other things clear*. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both record this double reading, and the Qur'an itself seems to intend both. It is clear: its language is intelligible, its commands are determinate, its distinctions between truth and falsehood are sharp. And it clarifies: it explains what human beings cannot work out for themselves.

> *"And We have sent down unto thee the Reminder, that thou mayest make clear to mankind what has been sent down to them, and haply they may reflect."* (Qur'an 16:44)

> *"We have sent down nothing upon you, [as] a Book, except that We have made clear in it all things, and as guidance and mercy and glad tidings for the submitters."* (cf. Qur'an 16:89)

There is a subtlety in the conjunction that the commentators noticed. The verse says "the signs of the Qur'an *and* a clear Book" — joining two nouns with *wa*, rather than saying "the signs of the clear Book." Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī explain that this gives two descriptions doing two different jobs: the first (*āyātu'l-Qurʾān*) describes the verses as proofs and evidences; the second (*kitāb mubīn*) describes the whole as an instrument of clarification. Proof and clarity are not the same thing. Something can be proven and remain obscure — a mathematical demonstration can be correct and incomprehensible. Something can be clear and remain unproven — a slogan. The Qur'an claims both: it is evidence *and* it is light, it establishes its case and it can be understood by the one hearing it. This is why the sūrah can move, without embarrassment, from the metaphysics of God's Throne to the practical warning of an ant.

**Clarity as a Theme Running Through the Whole Chapter**

The word *mubīn* and its root are not accidental here; they are the sūrah's own keynote, and the verse is placing a flag in the ground at the start. The same root returns at verse 13, where Pharaoh's people call Moses' signs *"siḥrun mubīn"* — "manifest sorcery": the identical adjective turned to the opposite use, clarity claimed by the enemies of clarity. It returns at verse 76, where the Qur'an is described as *"hudan wa raḥmatan li'l-muʾminīn"* and at verse 79 with *"al-ḥaqq al-mubīn"*, "the manifest truth." The sūrah is thus organized around a contest about what is *clear*: the Qur'an is clear, the miracle is clear, the truth is clear — and the disbelievers call the clear thing magic, and the clear thing fable (v. 68), and the clear thing a lie. The human problem, al-Naml keeps insisting, is not a shortage of evidence. It is a defect in the reception of evidence.

**Why a Sūrah About Ants Begins with Letters**

There is a fittingness between this opening and this chapter that a reader can feel without being able to prove. The sūrah will present a queen who does not know, an ant who does know, a bird who brings news from a land nobody has visited, a man who carries a throne in the time of a glance, and a people who will be shown signs and then know them. It is a sūrah about how knowledge arrives and how it is received. And it begins by making its reader stop — by putting two meaningless syllables at the threshold, so that the very first act of reading is an act of attention rather than of consumption. The letters accomplish before interpretation what the sūrah will argue at length: that the barrier to guidance is rarely the absence of a sign, and nearly always the absence of a pause.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:2

> **a guidance and glad tidings for the believers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Nouns Attached to the Book, and the Order Between Them**

Verse 2 continues the sentence begun in verse 1 — the two verses form one utterance split across a breath: *These are the signs of the Qur'an and a clear Book, a guidance and glad tidings for the believers.* Grammatically, *hudan wa bushrā* stands in the accusative, and the commentators read it either as a circumstantial qualification (*ḥāl*) — "in the state of being a guidance and glad tidings" — or as the predicate of an elided sentence: "[it is] a guidance and glad tidings." Either reading makes the same substantive point: the verse is stating what the Book *does*, not only what it *is*. Verse 1 named its nature (signs, clarity); verse 2 names its function (guidance, good news).

The word *hudan* comes from the root *h-d-y*, whose basic sense is to lead gently along a road — not to compel, not to carry, but to show the way so that the one guided walks it himself. Arabic distinguishes *h-d-y* from other verbs of leading: *sāqa* is to drive from behind, *qāda* is to pull from in front, and *hadā* is to guide with a light that the guided can see and follow. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī's lexical analysis, which the commentators rely on, notes that *hudā* is guidance to something *by means of something* — it always has an object and a method. The Qur'an is guidance: it is the method, and its object is what the rest of the verse-cluster will disclose.

**Guidance First, Joy Second: A Sequence with a Reason**

The order of the two nouns is not arbitrary, and both al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī comment on it. Guidance precedes glad tidings because glad tidings are the *result* of guidance, not a substitute for it. A message that began with reassurance and only later mentioned direction would be flattery; a message that gives direction and then reassures is medicine followed by news of recovery. The Qur'an is consistent about this ordering:

> *"Say: It is, for those who believe, a guidance and a healing."* (Qur'an 41:44)

> *"O mankind! There has come unto you an exhortation from your Lord, a healing for what is in the breasts, and a guidance and a mercy for the believers."* (Qur'an 10:57)

The pairing in our verse — *hudan wa bushrā* — appears also at 2:97, where Gabriel is described as bringing the Qur'an down *"upon thy heart by God's leave, confirming what was before it, a guidance and glad tidings for the believers"*, and at 16:102 in almost identical words. That the same phrase is used of the descent of the Book in two other places tells us something about how the Qur'an understands itself: it is not primarily a text of obligation. Its own self-description, repeated across Makkan and Madinan revelation, begins with *guidance* and ends with *good news*. The law comes within that frame, not outside it.

**Bushrā: The Word That Touches the Face**

*Bushrā* deserves attention as a word. Its root *b-sh-r* has to do with skin — *basharah* is the outer surface of the skin, the part of a human being that shows. From it comes *bashar*, mankind, named for the visible skin that distinguishes humans from animals covered in hide or fur. And from it comes *bashshara*, to give good news: the classical lexicons explain that the good news is so named because it *changes the face* — joy shows in the complexion, brightens the skin, opens the countenance. To give someone *bushrā* is to give them something that appears on their face before they can speak.

This is why the Qur'an reserves the word for a specific kind of announcement. It is used of the angels' message to Abraham and his wife (11:71, 51:28), of Zachariah's son (19:7), of Mary's son (19:30), of the Garden promised to the believers (2:25, 10:64), and of Paradise as the reward of striving (9:111, 61:13). It is never used for trivial good news. The glad tidings of verse 2 are, accordingly, the greatest possible news: that the road leads somewhere, that the account will be settled in mercy, that the Garden is real and is being prepared.

The psychological necessity of this is worth stating plainly. Human beings cannot sustain moral effort on the basis of duty alone; they need to believe that the effort terminates in something. A traveler who is told only that the road is long and steep, without being told what lies at the end, will sit down. The Qur'an's method is to make the destination vivid first and the demands second — which is why, in the ordering of the revelation itself, the Makkan sūrahs spent thirteen years establishing God, the Hereafter, and the Garden before the Madinan sūrahs legislated in detail. Verse 2 is that method in miniature: guidance, then joy, then (in verse 3) the specific obligations.

**"For the Believers": The Qualification That Puzzles Readers**

The verse ends with *li'l-muʾminīn* — "for the believers." To a modern reader this can seem circular: the Qur'an guides the people who are already guided, and brings good news to the people who already believe. The classical commentators answered the objection directly, and their answers are logically precise rather than defensive.

The first answer distinguishes two senses of guidance. There is guidance of *indication* (*hidāyat al-irshād* or *dalālah*), which is offered to everyone believer and unbeliever alike — the Qur'an is a "reminder to the worlds" (81:27) and the Prophet ﷺ is "a warner to the worlds" (25:1). And there is guidance of *granting success* (*hidāyat al-tawfīq*), by which a heart actually receives and acts upon what it has been shown — and this, the Qur'an says repeatedly, is God's alone: *"Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guideth whom He will"* (28:56). The verse specifies the believers because in them the second kind of guidance has begun to operate; the Book is doing to them what a Book can do.

The second answer is that faith and guidance increase each other, and the Qur'an states this explicitly:

> *"And as for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and gives them their reverence."* (Qur'an 47:17)

> *"And whenever a sūrah is revealed, there are some among them who say, 'Which of you has it increased in faith?' As for those who believe, it increases them in faith, and they rejoice."* (Qur'an 9:124)

Guidance is therefore not a possession acquired once and held; it is a relation that must be maintained and that grows. A lamp is light for the room it is in — and the room must keep its windows clean. This is why the same Qur'an that calls itself guidance *for the believers* also calls itself a means by which many are misled (2:26), and why it warns believers repeatedly not to let their hearts harden (57:16). The qualification in verse 2 is not an exclusion of others; it is an accurate description of where the light actually lands.

**Good News in an Age of Bad**

The practical bearing of this verse on a contemporary reader is considerable, and it becomes sharper by contrast with the environment in which most people now receive information. The modern information economy is overwhelmingly structured around *negative* news — because alarm captures attention faster than reassurance, and attention is what the economy trades in. A person can spend four hours a day absorbing catastrophe, danger, outrage, and collapse, and end the day with a settled conviction that the world is going to ruin and that nothing he does matters. The Qur'an's self-description is the deliberate inversion of that diet: it announces itself as *bushrā* — as the good news that appears on the face.

This is not naive optimism. Al-Naml contains the destruction of Thamūd, the burning of Lot's city, the beast of the end of the world, and faces cast down into the Fire. The Qur'an never hides the danger. But it insists on the *order* of the announcement: the danger is real, and so is the way out, and the way out is announced first as news before it is detailed as obligation. A believer who has internalized verse 2 approaches his own failures — his missed prayers, his broken fasts, his resentments, his debts — as matters for which good news exists, rather than as evidence of an unredeemable self. That single shift in posture is, in the estimation of every spiritual writer in the tradition from al-Ḥārith al-Muḥāsibī to Ibn ʿAṭāʾillāh, the difference between a religious life that produces growth and a religious life that produces despair. And despair, in that tradition, is not humility; it is a failure to believe verse 2.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:3

> **who perform the prayer and give the alms, and of the Hereafter are certain.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Verse 3 Defines the Believers of Verse 2**

Verse 2 promised guidance and glad tidings *for the believers*; verse 3 now says who those believers are. The method is characteristic of the Qur'an and worth noticing: it rarely leaves a category abstract. It names a group, and then in the next breath describes the group by what it does and what it holds to be true. Here the description has three elements — prayer, alms, and certainty about the Hereafter — and the three are not a random list. They map, respectively, onto the human being's relation to God, to other human beings, and to time. A person who prays has oriented himself toward his Creator; a person who gives alms has oriented himself toward his neighbor; a person who is certain of the Hereafter has oriented himself toward consequence. Take away any one of the three and the structure collapses: prayer without alms becomes a private performance, alms without prayer becomes secular philanthropy, and both without the Hereafter lose the only sanction that does not depend on how one feels today.

**"Who Establish the Prayer": The Verb of Standing Up**

The Arabic is *alladhīna yuqīmūna al-ṣalāh* — literally, "those who *raise up* / *establish* the prayer." The verb is not *yuṣallūna* ("they pray") but Form IV from the root *q-w-m*, to stand, to rise, to make something stand upright. The commentators are unanimous that the difference matters. To *perform* a prayer is to do an action; to *establish* the prayer is to build an institution inside one's own life — to make it stand at its appointed times, in its appointed form, persistently, until it becomes load-bearing. A pillar is not an event; it is a structure that continues to do its work when nobody is looking at it.

This distinction explains a great deal of ordinary religious experience. Many people pray when they feel like praying; the one who *establishes* prayer prays when the time comes, whether he feels like it or not. The Prophet ﷺ made the same distinction the hinge of his famous warning, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Buraydah:

> *"Whoever guards the five prayers will find them a light, a proof, and a deliverance on the Day of Resurrection. And whoever does not guard them will have neither light, nor proof, nor deliverance, and on the Day of Resurrection he will be with Qārūn, Pharaoh, Hāmān, and Ubayy ibn Khalaf."* (Aḥmad; al-Dārimī)

And on the prayer's structural place in the whole of religion, al-Bukhārī and Muslim record the Prophet's ﷺ summary:

> *"The matter is Islam, its pillar is the prayer, and its highest point is striving in God's way."* (al-Tirmidhī 2616, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The pairing of prayer and alms in a single phrase is one of the Qur'an's most persistent habits — the two appear together in dozens of places, among them 2:43, 2:83, 2:110, 2:177, 2:277, 4:77, 5:12, 5:55, 7:156, 9:5, 9:71, 19:31, 21:73, 22:41, 24:37, 24:56, 31:4, 33:33, 58:13, 73:20, and 98:5. Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq's argument to the Companions after the Prophet's ﷺ death — that he would fight anyone who separated what God had joined — rests entirely on this pairing, and it is transmitted in the two Ṣaḥīḥs from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"By God, I will fight whoever makes a distinction between the prayer and the alms."* (al-Bukhārī 1399; Muslim 20)

The logic is plain once stated: prayer is the acknowledgment that God owns you; alms is the acknowledgment that God owns what is in your hand. A person who claims the first while refusing the second is not humble, he is selective.

**"And Give the Alms": Purification Named After Growth**

*Zakāh* comes from the root *z-k-w*, which carries two meanings held together in the single word: growth and purity. A plant that *yazkū* is one that grows and flourishes; a person who is *zakiyy* is one who is pure. The technical religious meaning — the annual levy on qualifying wealth, distributed to the eight categories named at 9:60 — is built on both. The payment *purifies* the payer, because it removes from his wealth the share that was never morally his alone; and it *grows* what remains, because wealth that has been purified is wealth that can be used without corruption. The Qur'an states the double effect in a single command:

> *"Take alms from their wealth, whereby thou mayest purify them and make them grow, and pray for them. Truly thy prayer is a reassurance for them."* (Qur'an 9:103)

It is instructive that the one act which *reduces* a person's assets is called, in Arabic, "growth." This is not wordplay; it is a statement of the sūrah's economic metaphysic, which will be developed at verse 40 with Solomon's throne and at verse 73 with God's bounty. What you keep for yourself alone stagnates; what you release under God's command increases. Anyone who has watched a family's fortune destroyed by greed, or a modest business thrive because its owner was known to be honest and generous, has seen the verse operating in ordinary life.

**"Of the Hereafter Are Certain": The Word Repeated for Emphasis**

The final clause is *wa hum bi'l-ākhirati hum yūqinūn* — "and they, of the Hereafter, they are certain." The Arabic contains a feature that translations flatten: the pronoun *hum* ("they") appears twice, framing the phrase *bi'l-ākhirah* ("of the Hereafter"). Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī explain this as one of the strongest available forms of emphasis in Arabic syntax — what grammarians call *faṣl* combined with repetition of the subject — and its effect in English would be something like: "and as for the Hereafter, *they* — they are the ones who are certain of it." The verse is not merely reporting a belief; it is marking these people out by it.

*Yūqinūn* is from the root *y-q-n*, giving *yaqīn*: certainty. Arabic has a graded vocabulary for knowledge, and the tradition arranged it in ascending stages: *ʿilm* (knowledge), *ʿayn al-yaqīn* (certainty as though seeing), *ḥaqq al-yaqīn* (certainty as reality itself). The famous illustration, attributed in the commentaries to Ibn ʿAbbās and developed by al-Ghazālī, uses smoke and fire: to know from smoke that there is a fire is *ʿilm al-yaqīn*; to see the fire itself is *ʿayn al-yaqīn*; to be warmed or burned by it is *ḥaqq al-yaqīn*. The believer described in verse 3 does not merely assent to the proposition that there is a Hereafter; his conviction has reached the level at which it reorganizes behavior. That is the practical test of *yaqīn* throughout the Qur'an: certainty is what you act on.

The identical phrase appears at 2:4, describing those for whom al-Baqarah is a guidance — *"and who are certain of the Hereafter"* — and at 31:4, in a verse so similar to ours that the commentators treat the two as twins:

> *"who establish the prayer and give the alms, and are certain of the Hereafter."* (Qur'an 31:4)

That the Qur'an uses this formula in a Makkan sūrah (31:4), in a Madinan one (2:4), and here again (27:3) indicates that it considered this triad — prayer, alms, eschatological certainty — the irreducible definition of a believer. Everything else is elaboration.

**Why the Hereafter Is Named as Certainty Rather Than Knowledge**

There is a precise theological reason for the word choice. The Hereafter cannot be *known* in the ordinary sense: no one has returned from it with a report, no instrument has measured it, and no argument can compel it. It can only be *believed with certainty* — and the Qur'an names that state *yaqīn* rather than *ʿilm* because *yaqīn* is a certainty held without direct evidence, sustained by trust in the One who reported it. This makes the Hereafter the hardest single article of faith, and it is why the sūrah will return to it again and again: at verse 4, where those who do *not* believe in the Hereafter have their deeds adorned for them; at verse 5, where they are named the greatest losers; at verses 65–68, where the disbelievers ask sarcastically whether they will be brought back when they are dust; at verse 87, where the trumpet is blown.

The connection between verse 3 and verse 4 is therefore not a list followed by a digression. It is a diagnosis followed by its opposite case. Certainty of the Hereafter is what makes prayer consistent and alms possible — because both require you to pay now for a return you will not see now. Remove that certainty and the entire moral structure becomes irrational: why stand at dawn to pray when you could sleep, why give away a fortieth of your wealth when you could invest it? The answer is that you only do those things if you are *certain* of a settlement. And those who lack that certainty, verse 4 will say, do not become neutral observers of life — they become people whose own deeds are made to seem beautiful to them while they wander blind. The two verses are one argument.

**The Order of the Three: A Curriculum, Not a Catalogue**

It is worth asking why the verse gives prayer first, alms second, and certainty last, when logically the certainty is the foundation of the other two. The commentators offer a subtle answer: the verse is describing not the order of *acquisition* but the order of *visibility*. Prayer is the most visible act of the believer — performed five times daily, in fixed form, often in company. Alms is less visible but still external, touching property and other people. Certainty is entirely internal, invisible to everyone including sometimes its owner. The verse moves from outside to inside, as though leading the reader inward: here is what these people do; here is what they give; and beneath both, here is what they hold. A reader who examines his own life in that order — my practice, my generosity, my conviction — will usually discover that the weakest item is the last one, which is why it is the one the verse emphasizes with a doubled pronoun.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:4

> **Truly those who believe not in the Hereafter, We have made their deeds seem fair unto them, while they wander confused.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counter-Portrait Begins Where the Portrait Ended**

Verses 2–3 described the believers in three strokes; verse 4 turns and describes their opposite in two. The transition is abrupt and grammatically emphatic: *inna* — "truly, indeed" — the particle of confirmation that Arabic uses when a statement will be resisted or doubted. The Qur'an does not say "those who disbelieve in the Hereafter"; it says *inna'lladhīna lā yuʾminūna bi'l-ākhirah* — "truly those who do not believe in the Hereafter." The emphasis is placed on the very article of faith named as the third mark of the believer in the preceding verse. The two verses are a matched pair, and the sūrah is telling us that everything that follows in it — Moses, Solomon, Bilqīs, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, the beast, the trumpet — is addressed to the difference between them.

**"We Have Made Their Deeds Seem Fair": The Verb and Its Three Agents**

The central phrase is *zayyannā lahum aʿmālahum* — "We have adorned for them their deeds" — from the root *z-y-n*, to beautify, adorn, decorate, make attractive. The same root gives *zīnah*, ornament and finery, and the Qur'an uses it of the beautification of the heavens with stars (37:6, 41:12, 67:5) as well as of the beautification of evil in human eyes.

The theological difficulty in the phrase is real and the commentators faced it rather than explained it away. The Qur'an attributes the adornment of evil to three different agents in three different registers. Sometimes God is the actor, as here and at 6:108, 6:122, 6:137, 8:48, 9:37, 16:63, 29:38, 35:8, and 40:37. Sometimes Satan is the actor, as at 15:39 (*"I will surely adorn [evil] for them on the earth"*) and 8:48 in its second half. And sometimes the verb appears in the passive voice with no agent named, as at 9:37: *"the evil of their deeds is made to seem fair unto them."*

Al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī, and Ibn Taymiyyah all work out the reconciliation on the same principle, and it is a principle of divine justice rather than of divine caprice. God's act of adorning is not the beginning of the story but its *consequence*. The sequence runs: a person chooses, repeatedly, to prefer the immediate over the true; God then withdraws the corrective light that would have made the immediate look as bad as it is; and the withdrawal of correction is itself described as God's act, because in a universe where God is the only real agent, nothing happens outside His permission and will. The Qur'an states the sequence explicitly:

> *"And as for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance... And when they swerved, God made their hearts swerve."* (Qur'an 47:17; 61:5)

> *"And they denied them — though their souls were convinced of them — wrongfully and exultantly."* (Qur'an 27:14 — this very sūrah, ten verses later)

Verse 14 is the key to verse 4, and the commentators treat them together: Pharaoh's people *already knew* the signs were true; their rejection came from *wrongdoing and exultation*. Only after that does the adornment operate. So the rule is: God adorns a person's evil deeds for him *after* the person has chosen them. The decoration is not a deception imposed on an innocent mind; it is the confirmation of a preference already made. Ibn Taymiyyah puts the same point in the language of causation and justice: God's creating an act in a servant does not remove the servant's having willed it, and God's permitting an evil to look attractive does not excuse the one who wanted it to look attractive.

**Yaʿmahūn: A Blindness Worse Than Blindness**

The verse ends with *fa hum yaʿmahūn* — "so they wander confused" or "they stagger in bewilderment." The verb is from *ʿ-m-h*, and the distinction between it and the ordinary word for blindness (*ʿ-m-y*) is important and was carefully drawn by the lexicographers. *ʿAmā* is blindness of the eye; *ʿamah* is blindness of the heart and mind — the state of a person who cannot tell where he is going, who oscillates, who staggers. Al-Rāghib defines *ʿamah* as bewilderment in the direction of one's affair, such that a person cannot find his way in it. The image is of a man in open country at night who has lost the path and keeps turning — sometimes walking toward his destination, sometimes away from it, never knowing which.

The same verb appears in the Qur'an's description of the hypocrites of Madinah:

> *"Those are they who have purchased error at the price of guidance, so their commerce has not profited, nor were they guided... God mocks them and leaves them in their insolence, blindly wandering (yaʿmahūn)."* (Qur'an 2:15–16)

And in three further places: *"We shall turn their hearts and their sights, just as they did not believe in it the first time, and We shall leave them in their insolence, blindly wandering"* (6:110); *"And whomsoever God sends astray, He leaves them to wander blindly in their insolence"* (7:186); and of the disbelievers on the Day of Judgment, *"We shall gather them on their faces, blind, dumb, and deaf"* (17:97). The word carries, in every instance, the sense of an *active, continuing* disorientation rather than a static condition: these people are not merely lost, they are busy being lost.

Al-Qushayrī, cited in the source commentary, connects this wandering to attachment: the state results directly from one's attachment to ephemeral objects, which renders a person incapable of the spiritual life. The logic is worth unfolding, because it is a psychological observation of the first order. A person whose attention is entirely invested in things that change hourly — approval, novelty, appetite, status — cannot form a stable intention, because a stable intention requires a stable object. He is therefore perpetually in motion and perpetually undecided, which is exactly what *ʿamah* means. The modern reader has a precise analogue: the state of a person who has spent six hours scrolling and cannot afterwards say what he wanted, what he believes, or why he is tired.

**Why Adornment Is the Specific Punishment for Denying the Hereafter**

The pairing of crime and penalty in this verse is not accidental, and the commentators noted its exactness. The crime is disbelief in the Hereafter — that is, the refusal to accept that present actions have future consequences. The penalty is that present actions look permanently good to the one performing them. The two fit together as diagnosis and symptom: if you will not admit that there is a settlement, then there is nothing left to correct your appraisal of the moment, and so the moment will always justify itself.

This is the mechanism behind every addiction and every ruined career, and it is why the verse is not merely about seventh-century Arabian pagans. The person who embezzles does not experience himself as a thief at the moment of transfer; he experiences himself as clever, as temporarily bridging a gap, as someone who will fix it later. The person who destroys a marriage does not experience himself as a destroyer; he experiences himself as finally being honest about his needs, as someone who deserves this. The person who ruins his health does not experience himself as dying slowly; he experiences himself as enjoying a Tuesday. In every case, the deed has been *adorned* — made to look fair — and in every case the adornment was available because there was no settled conviction of an accounting. The Qur'an's claim is not that such people are unusually wicked. It is that they have removed the only instrument that could have told them otherwise, and that God has confirmed the removal.

**The Political and Social Dimension of Adorned Deeds**

There is a further implication that the commentators drew from the plural in *aʿmālahum*, "their deeds." The adornment is collective as well as individual, and societies are adorned to themselves in the same way persons are. The Qur'an describes entire civilizations in this condition — the people of Noah, of ʿĀd, of Thamūd, of Pharaoh, of Lot — and in each case the collective self-image was one of strength, sophistication, and permanence:

> *"And they said: 'Who is greater in might than we?'"* (Qur'an 41:15)

> *"They said: 'We are more [in numbers] and more in wealth and children, and we will not be punished.'"* (Qur'an 34:35)

A society that no longer believes it will be judged cannot correct itself, because correction requires the admission that a current practice is wrong — and the practice has been adorned. This is why the Qur'an's warnings about *fasād*, corruption in the land, so often accompany its warnings about the Hereafter, and why this sūrah will describe Thamūd's nine conspirators precisely as men who "worked corruption in the land and did not set matters aright" (v. 48). The verse is thus a diagnostic tool that a reader can apply to his own civilization: look at the practices that everyone considers normal, prosperous, and beyond criticism — and ask whether the consensus is knowledge or adornment.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:5

> **It is they who shall have a terrible punishment, and in the Hereafter it is they who are the greatest losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence Completes the Diagnosis With a Verdict**

Verse 4 described a condition; verse 5 states its consequence. The demonstrative *ulāʾika* — "those" — reaches back over the whole preceding description and gathers it into a single group: the people whose deeds have been adorned and who wander confused. *Lahum sūʾu'l-ʿadhāb* — "for them is the worst of punishment" or "a terrible punishment." The noun *sūʾ* comes from the root *s-w-ʾ*, meaning badness, evil, that which is displeasing and harmful; Arabic uses it for anything that offends the senses or the soul, including the "evil eye" and an "evil deed." The construct *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb* is thus "the evil/badness of the punishment" — its most painful and degrading aspect.

**Two Punishments, or One Punishment in Two Worlds**

The commentators divided on the scope of the phrase, and the division is informative. Al-Rāzī held that *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb* can refer to any punishment in this world or the Hereafter — the disgrace of being wrong, the loss of peace, the internal wandering of verse 4 itself, and the external punishments that overtook the Quraysh. Others, including al-Zamakhsharī, situated the first punishment in this world specifically and identified it with the Battle of Badr, where the leadership of Makkan paganism was killed or captured within a few years of this sūrah's revelation. Ibn Kathīr follows this reading, noting the Qur'an's own promise:

> *"Say unto those who disbelieve: 'You will be overcome and gathered unto Hell — an evil resting place.'"* (Qur'an 3:12)

The second half of the verse then explicitly locates the greater loss in the Hereafter: *wa hum fī'l-ākhirati humu'l-akhsarūn* — "and they, in the Hereafter, they are the greatest losers." Note the same doubled pronoun construction used in verse 3 for certainty about the Hereafter: *wa hum bi'l-ākhirati hum yūqinūn*. The Qur'an is building a deliberate symmetry — the very structure of the sentence that praised the believers for their certainty about the Hereafter is reused in the sentence that condemns the disbelievers in the Hereafter. Certainty about it is the mark of the saved; loss in it is the mark of the ruined. The same phrase, the same emphasis, the opposite content.

**Al-Akhsarūn: The Language of Commerce in the Qur'an**

*Akhsar* is the elative of *khāsir*, from the root *kh-s-r*, to lose, to come out behind in a transaction, to have one's capital diminished. The Qur'an's moral vocabulary is deeply commercial, and this is one of its most sustained metaphors. Human life is a transaction; a soul is capital; deeds are merchandise; the Hereafter is the settlement day; the disbeliever is the merchant who bought a worthless thing at the price of everything he had.

> *"Those are they who have purchased error at the price of guidance, so their commerce has not profited them, nor were they guided."* (Qur'an 2:16)

> *"O you who believe! Shall I direct you to a commerce that will deliver you from a painful punishment?"* (Qur'an 61:10)

> *"Verily, God has purchased from the believers their selves and their wealth, in exchange for which theirs shall be the Garden."* (Qur'an 9:111)

> *"By the declining day, truly man is in loss (khusr), save those who believe and work righteousness, and exhort one another to truth, and exhort one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

> *"a commerce that will never fail"* (Qur'an 35:29)

Against this whole vocabulary, al-Ṭabarī explains the phrase *al-akhsarūn* — the *greatest* losers — by reference to the two-worlds structure of the transaction. Some people lose the world and gain the Hereafter: the poor believer, the persecuted Muslim of Makkah, the one who gave away his property for the sake of God. Some people gain the world and lose the Hereafter. But the people described here lose both: they are punished in this world, as verse 4 already showed, by the wretchedness of their own adorned wandering, and they lose the Hereafter absolutely. A merchant who loses his goods and his ship and his reputation and his life has not merely lost; he has lost in the superlative. Hence *akhsar*, not *khāsir*.

**Why the Loss Is Called "Greatest" and Not Simply "Total"**

There is a further point of precision in the word choice, and it repays attention. The Qur'an elsewhere describes total loss with other formulas — *"their works have come to naught"* (18:104), *"We shall assign no weight to their deeds"* (18:105). Here it uses the comparative-superlative form, which in Arabic implies a comparison with other losers. Al-Qurṭubī draws the comparison explicitly: among those who lose, there are grades. The believer who sins loses in proportion to his sin but retains his capital — his faith — and will come out. The disbeliever loses the capital itself. And the one described here is called *akhsar* because he was given more: he had received the signs, he had heard the messenger, he had been shown the clear Book — and he had his own deeds adorned over all of it. Loss is measured against what was at stake, and in this case the stake was the whole of revelation.

The psychological truth underneath the commercial metaphor is worth stating for a reader who finds accounting language cold. Every human being spends the one resource he cannot earn more of — the hours of his life — and spends it on things. Verse 5's claim is that most people do not know what they are buying. The man who trades twenty years for a position discovers at fifty that the position did not contain what he thought; the woman who trades her youth for approval discovers that approval evaporates; the believer who trades a portion of his comfort for prayer discovers at the end that the prayer was the only thing that stayed. The verse does not say that disbelievers are stupid. It says that they are the greatest losers — and the reason, given in verse 4, is that their appraisals had been decorated for them. They overpaid because they could not see the price.

**"In the Hereafter": The Location of the Final Accounting**

The prepositional phrase *fī'l-ākhirah* — "in the Hereafter" — fixes the location of the verdict, and that fixing is itself a mercy and a warning at once. It is a mercy because it means this world is not the place of final judgment: the wicked are not punished fully here, and the righteous are not rewarded fully here, which is precisely why the world looks unjust and why the Hereafter is necessary. It is a warning because it means nothing here is final either — not the triumph of the oppressor, not the suffering of the believer, not the apparent success of a life built on adorned deeds. The settlement is elsewhere, and it is certain.

This is the same point the sūrah will make at verse 89–90 with its own accounting language — *"Whosoever brings a good deed, he will have that which is better than it... And whosoever brings an evil deed, their faces shall be cast down into the Fire"* — and at verse 75, where nothing hidden in heaven or earth is absent from a clear Book. Al-Naml is, in its structure, a sūrah about ledgers: the ledger of Solomon's gratitude, the ledger of Thamūd's conspiracy, the ledger of the ant's warning, and finally the ledger that will be brought out on the Day when the beast speaks. Verse 5 is the first entry in that last one.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:6

> **Truly thou dost receive the Quran from the Presence of One who is Wise, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About the Origin of Everything the Sūrah Will Say**

After six verses describing the Book, its recipients, and its rejecters, the sūrah turns to address its bearer directly. *Wa innaka la-tulaqqā'l-Qurʾāna min ladun ḥakīmin ʿalīm* — "And truly thou art made to receive the Qur'an from the Presence of One Wise, Knowing." The double emphasis of *inna* plus the *lām* of confirmation (*la-tulaqqā*) is among the strongest assertions available in Arabic: "and you — indeed you — are certainly receiving." Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both note that this verse functions as an introduction to the prophetic narratives that follow: before the sūrah tells the story of Moses, of Solomon, of Ṣāliḥ and Lot, it establishes who is doing the telling and where the telling comes from.

**Tulaqqā: A Word of Receiving, Not of Composing**

The verb *tulaqqā* is from the root *l-q-y*, to meet, to encounter, to receive. Form II of this root gives *laqqā*, to make someone meet something, to teach by dictation, to instill — and it is the root from which *talqīn* comes, the prompt given to a reciter, and *liqāʾ*, the meeting with God Himself. The passive form used here, *tulaqqā*, means "you are made to receive" or "it is conveyed to you." The choice of verb is theologically loaded and deliberately so.

The Qur'an never uses, of itself and the Prophet ﷺ, a verb of making, composing, or producing. It uses verbs of *descent* and *reception*: *nazzala* (He sent down), *anzala* (He revealed), *awḥā* (He inspired), *alqā* (He cast), *talaqqā* (he received). Adam received words from his Lord (*fa-talaqqā Ādamu min rabbihi kalimāt*, 2:37) — the same root as our verse. And the Qur'an states the point as an argument against those who accused the Prophet ﷺ of authorship:

> *"And thou didst not recite before it any book, nor didst thou transcribe one with thy right hand; for then the falsifiers might have doubted."* (Qur'an 29:48)

> *"Say: 'It is not my own to change it of my own accord. I follow only what is revealed unto me. Truly I fear, should I disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.'"* (Qur'an 10:15)

> *"And when Our clear signs are recited unto them, those who hope not to meet Us say, 'Bring a Qur'an other than this, or change it.' Say: 'It is not for me to change it of my own accord.'"* (Qur'an 10:15)

The Prophet ﷺ described his own role in the plainest terms, in a report recorded by al-Bukhārī in the context of the conquest narrative and cited throughout the commentaries:

> *"I have been given the jawāmiʿ al-kalim — the comprehensive words — and I was helped by terror, and while I slept the treasures of the earth were brought to me and placed in my two hands."* (al-Bukhārī 2977; Muslim 523)

*Jawāmiʿ al-kalim* — speech in which few words carry many meanings — is a description of a capacity granted, not acquired. The verse's verb is consistent with it: he *receives*.

**Min Ladun: The Preposition of Proximity Without Direction**

*Min ladun* — "from the presence of" — is one of the Qur'an's most distinctive prepositional phrases, used exclusively of what comes from God and always with the sense of an unmediated nearness that no spatial language can capture. It appears in the prayer of the People of the Cave: *"and grant us from Thy presence (min ladunka) a mercy"* (18:10); in the story of Moses and al-Khiḍr, where the latter is described as one *"whom We had taught knowledge from Our presence (ʿilman min ladunnā)"* (18:65); and of Zachariah's granted son (3:38, 19:5, 21:90).

The commentators emphasize that *ladun* implies closeness without implying location, because God is not in a place. Al-Rāzī draws out the consequence: the phrase tells us that the Qur'an's origin is not a distance to be traversed but a presence to be met. And there is a chain in the descent which the Qur'an itself specifies elsewhere, and which verse 6 assumes:

> *"Say: 'Whoever is an enemy to Gabriel — it is he who brought it down upon thy heart by God's leave, confirming what was before it, a guidance and glad tidings for the believers.'"* (Qur'an 2:97)

> *"And truly it is a revelation from the Lord of the worlds, which the Trustworthy Spirit brought down upon thy heart, that thou mayest be among the warners, in a clear Arabic tongue."* (Qur'an 26:192–195)

Notice that the preceding sūrah, al-Shuʿarāʾ, ended its account of the descent with those verses — and al-Naml begins, six verses in, by re-announcing the same descent from the other end: not the Spirit bringing it down, but the servant receiving it. The two sūrahs are joined at this seam.

**Ḥakīm ʿAlīm: The Two Names and Their Order**

The verse closes with two Divine Names: *ḥakīmin ʿalīm*. Both are from the most frequently paired names in the Qur'an, but here the order is reversed from the more common *ʿalīm ḥakīm*. *Al-Ḥakīm* is from the root *ḥ-k-m*, which carries the sense of restraining, deciding, and placing things where they belong; wisdom (*ḥikmah*) in Arabic is not merely intelligence but the *right placement* of a thing — putting the appropriate act in the appropriate circumstance for the appropriate end. *Al-ʿAlīm* is from *ʿ-l-m*, knowledge in its fullest and most comprehensive sense, including what is hidden, what is future, and what is inside hearts.

The pairing of the two is a complete statement of the fitness of revelation. Knowledge without wisdom would produce information; wisdom without knowledge would produce good intentions with bad results. A physician who knows everything about the body but does not know how to treat this patient is useless; a physician who has good judgment but no knowledge is dangerous. God is named here as both: the One who knows all things and who places them rightly.

Al-Rāzī observes a further precision in the reversed order. Wisdom is mentioned first because it is what the recipient of the Qur'an encounters *first* — the fit of its laws to human nature, the aptness of its stories to human situations, the rightness of its prohibitions. Knowledge is mentioned second as the ground that makes wisdom possible. In other words: you meet the wisdom, and behind the wisdom stands the knowledge. This is exactly the order in which an honest reader encounters the Qur'an. He does not first verify God's omniscience and then accept the Book; he first finds the Book's guidance astonishingly apt for his own life, and from that aptness infers the Knower behind it.

**The Sufi Reading: Hearing the Qur'an "From God"**

The source commentary records a meditation from the Sufi tradition (cited as Aj, Ibn ʿAjībah) that extends the verse beyond its narrative context. On this reading, no one is counted among *al-rāsikhūn fi'l-ʿilm* — "those firmly rooted in knowledge," of whom 3:7 speaks — until he recites the Qur'an *bi'Llāh*, "through God," and hears it *min Allāh*, "from God": that is, hears it as coming from the Presence of One Wise, Knowing.

The practical content of that reading is a discipline of attention rather than a metaphysical claim. Most people read the Qur'an as a text about God; the *rāsikhūn* read it as speech *from* God addressed to them. The difference is the difference between reading a letter about someone and reading a letter from someone. Al-Ghazālī formulates the same discipline in the *Iḥyāʾ* when he advises the reciter to suppose that he hears each verse being addressed to him directly at that moment — that the commands are for him, the prohibitions for him, the promises to him, the warnings to him, and the stories about him insofar as his own soul repeats their pattern. That is what "thou dost receive the Qur'an from the Presence of One Wise, Knowing" means as an instruction rather than as a report: the Prophet ﷺ received it as present speech, and every reader who reads it as present speech is, in a limited but real sense, receiving it too.

**Why This Verse Comes Before the Story of Moses**

The placement of verse 6 immediately before the Mosaic narrative is not incidental, and the commentators' observation that it is "an introduction to the stories of the prophets that follow" deserves development. Everything the sūrah is about to narrate — a fire in the desert, a speaking bush, a staff becoming a serpent, a queen's throne transported across Arabia, an ant issuing a public warning, a bird reporting on a foreign kingdom, nine men assassinated by their own plot, a city destroyed by rain — is, on the face of it, impossible. A reader approaching such material with modern expectations will classify it as legend. Verse 6 is positioned to forestall that classification by fixing the source before the content: *whoever is telling you this is Wise and Knowing.*

The logical structure is that of a witness's credibility. When a person whose judgment you trust in a hundred verifiable matters reports something you cannot verify, you have three options: reject the report, reject the witness, or suspend the report pending further evidence. The Qur'an's argument throughout is that the witness's credibility has been established by everything else he says — its consistency, its moral fitness, its linguistic impossibility, its predictions, its knowledge of matters the reporter could not have known. Verse 6 asks the reader to hold that credibility in mind during the narratives that follow. It is, in effect, the sūrah saying: before I tell you what happened at the fire, remember who is telling you.

And for the ordinary believer the verse has a simpler daily use. When a command in the Qur'an or the Sunnah strikes him as strange — a prohibition he does not feel, a rule whose purpose he cannot see, a story he cannot imagine — verse 6 supplies the posture: this comes from One Wise, Knowing. Wisdom may be invisible to me while remaining intact in itself; knowledge may exceed me while remaining complete in Him. The verse thereby makes room for obedience without comprehension, which is not the suspension of reason but the recognition of its limits.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:7

> **[Remember] when Moses said unto his family, "Verily, I perceive a fire. I shall bring you some news therefrom, or a brand, that haply you may warm yourselves."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Origin of the Book to the Origin of a Prophet**

Verse 6 established that the Qur'an is received from a Wise and Knowing Presence. Verse 7 begins the demonstration by returning to the moment at which a previous prophet received his commission. The transition is signaled by the particle *idh* — "when" — which the translation renders "[Remember] when": a construction the Qur'an uses repeatedly to lift a scene out of narrative time and set it before the hearer as something to be witnessed rather than merely reported (cf. 2:30, *"and [remember] when thy Lord said unto the angels"*; 3:42; 5:110; 19:16). The story is not being told; it is being shown.

The scene is familiar from Exodus and from the Qur'an's own accounts at 20:9–24, 28:29–35, and 79:15–19. Moses has left Midian with his family after ten years of service to his father-in-law; he has lost the road in the dark and the cold; and he sees a fire at the side of the valley. What al-Naml adds to the other accounts is a detail of domestic tenderness — the promise to bring back *news* or a *brand* — and a compression of the whole calling into a handful of verses. The other accounts linger; this one moves.

**Ahl: The Word for Family, and What It Includes**

The Arabic is *idh qāla Mūsā li-ahlihī* — "when Moses said to his family." The word *ahl* means the people belonging to a person: family, household, and by extension the people of a place (*ahl al-Madīnah*, "the people of the city") or of a book (*ahl al-Kitāb*, "the People of the Book"). Al-Rāzī notes that here *ahl* may refer to Moses' wife alone — which is one legitimate sense of the word — while others take it to include his household and traveling companions. The Qur'an does not name Moses' wife in this passage, though it names her father's household elsewhere and identifies the marriage at 28:27–28.

The mention of family at the opening of a prophetic commission is not incidental. Throughout the Qur'an, revelation arrives to men inside their domestic lives, and the prophets are consistently portrayed with wives, children, and households — Abraham arguing for his father, Lot losing his wife, Zachariah praying for a son, Muhammad ﷺ receiving the first revelation and being wrapped in a cloak by Khadījah. The contrast with other religious literatures, in which the holy man is often a solitary ascetic removed from family, is deliberate. Prophethood in the Qur'an is not an escape from human relations but a commission given inside them; and the first thing Moses does when he sees the fire is think of his family's cold.

**"I Perceive a Fire": The Word That Means More Than Seeing**

*Innī ānastu nāran* — "Verily, I perceive a fire." The verb *ānastu* is Form IV from *ʾ-n-s*, a root whose field of meaning is intimacy, familiarity, and the feeling of being at ease with something: *ins* is mankind, named for its sociability; *uns* is the comfort one feels with a companion; *istānasa* is to seek familiarity or permission before entering. When Arabic says *ānastu*, it does not mean merely "I saw"; it means "I perceived something that gave me a sense of presence or familiarity" — I caught sight of something and my heart responded to it.

The commentators draw a careful conclusion from the word choice: Moses did not merely register a light on the horizon; he felt, in perceiving it, that there was someone there. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr note that the fire was in fact not fire at all — Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said it was a flickering light, and in another statement he called it "the Light of the Lord of the worlds." The word *nār* (fire) is what Moses used because that is what it appeared to be, and Arabs sometimes used *nār* in place of *nūr* (light). The Qur'an preserves his word rather than correcting it, because the verse is reporting *his perception* — and the point is that what looked like a fire to a lost traveler was in reality a theophany.

**Two Promises: News or a Brand**

Moses offers his family two things, and the alternatives are joined by *aw* — "or": *sa-ātīkum minhā bi-khabarin aw ātīkum bi-shihābin qabasin laʿallakum taṣṭalūn* — "I shall bring you from it some news, or a brand, a burning piece, that haply you may warm yourselves."

*Khobar*, news, is understood by Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī to mean information about the road, since the party had become lost. This is the practical hope of a traveler: where there is a fire there are people, and where there are people there is information. *Shihāb qabas* is a torch or a firebrand — *qabas* is a piece taken from a larger fire, the portion carried away; the same root gives *iqtibās*, to borrow light or knowledge from another. And *taṣṭalūn* is Form VIII from *ṣ-l-y*, to warm oneself at a fire — the same root, strikingly, that gives *ṣalāh* (prayer) in one of its etymological explanations, and *yaṣlā* (he will be scorched), used of the Fire of Hell. Arabic holds these meanings together deliberately.

The two promises correspond to the two needs of a lost family: to know where they are, and to be warm. And the sūrah will show that Moses gets neither of the two things he went for, and instead gets what he did not ask for. He goes for directions and receives a commission; he goes for a torch and receives a staff that becomes a serpent and a hand that shines white. This pattern — a person setting out for a small thing and being given a great one — is one of the Qur'an's most repeated narrative structures, and it is the pattern of Moses' life twice over: he fled Egypt for killing a man and returns to it as a prophet; he stops at a well in Midian to help two girls and ends up married and employed for a decade.

**Laʿalla: The Particle of Hope, Not of Certainty**

The clause ends with *laʿallakum taṣṭalūn* — "that haply, perhaps, you may warm yourselves." The particle *laʿalla* expresses hope or expectation, not guarantee. Its presence in a prophet's speech about a trivial matter is a small and precise piece of characterization: Moses does not promise his family fire; he says he will try to bring it. He is a man who has been lost in the desert for a night with dependents relying on him, and he does not overstate. The Qur'an's portraits of its prophets are notable for exactly this kind of detail — Abraham's hesitation, Jacob's grief, Jonah's impatience, Muhammad's ﷺ anxiety that his people not believe — and the effect is that the reader is not being given icons but men.

**The Sufi Reading: Three Stages of Certainty in One Fire**

The source commentary records a meditation, from the Sufi tradition, that reads this verse as a map of the stages of certainty — the same three stages named in the Qur'an itself at 102:5–7 (*ʿilm al-yaqīn*, *ʿayn al-yaqīn*, *ḥaqq al-yaqīn*). On this reading: knowing *about* the fire is theoretical knowledge of spiritual realities; *seeing* the fire is direct vision; and being *consumed* by the fire is the transformation of the soul's substance by the Truth.

The reading is an allegory, and the commentators who offer it do not claim it displaces the plain sense — al-Naml's own method, as we will see at verse 44 with Ibn ʿArabī and Ibn ʿAjībah, is to allow the plain sense to stand while drawing out its spiritual resonance. The allegory is nonetheless coherent with the verse's own structure. Moses at a distance knows the fire exists (knowledge); he walks toward it and sees it (vision); he arrives and is addressed from within it (transformation). And verse 3 of this sūrah had just described the believers as people *of certainty* — so the very next narrative the sūrah tells is of a man walking toward certainty through its stages. The reader who has been told what certainty is now watches a prophet acquire it.

**The Ordinary Setting of Extraordinary Events**

Finally, the verse's practical lesson lies in its setting. The greatest commission in the history of the Children of Israel begins with a cold family, a lost road, and a man going to fetch a torch. Nothing about the scene is religious: there is no temple, no ritual, no fast, no vow. There is a need and a willingness to meet it. The Qur'an is repeatedly insistent that God's calls arrive inside ordinary human situations — to Mary while she is secluded from her family, to Zachariah while he stands in prayer, to Muhammad ﷺ while he is alone in a cave he had gone to for quiet, to Moses while he is doing an errand for his wife and children.

The implication for a reader is that the search for God is not a separate department of life to be entered at convenient intervals. It happens on the way to something else. The person who is cold, lost, tired, and responsible for others, and who nonetheless gets up and walks toward the only light he can see, is already in the posture that verse 7 describes — and the history of prophecy suggests that this is the posture in which light tends to speak.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:8

> **Then when he came to it, a call came unto him, "Blessed is the One in the fire, and the one around it. And glory be to God, Lord of the worlds!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arrival, and the Sentence That Has No Parallel in the Qur'an**

*Falammā jāʾahā nūdiya an būrika man fi'l-nāri wa man ḥawlahā* — "Then when he came to it, he was called: Blessed is the One in the fire, and the one around it." This is one of the most difficult and most discussed sentences in the entire Qur'an, and the commentators from al-Ṭabarī to al-Rāzī to Ibn Kathīr devote more space to it than to almost any other single clause in the Mosaic narratives. The difficulty is not linguistic but theological: the sentence appears to place God *in* a fire, and Islamic creed holds categorically that God is not in a place, not a body, not contained, and not comparable to anything.

> *"There is nothing like unto Him, and He is the Hearing, the Seeing."* (Qur'an 42:11)

Every reading of the verse is therefore also a statement about how Muslims handle language that appears to imply bodily location in God. The verse is, in effect, a test case for the whole discipline of interpreting the Divine Attributes.

**The Grammatical Problem: A Verb Without an Object**

The source commentary identifies the technical root of the ambiguity. The verb *bāraka* — to bless — ordinarily takes a direct or indirect object: one blesses *someone* or *something*, and God blesses a person or a place. Here the verb appears in the passive form *būrika*, "blessed is," followed by *man*, "the one who / that which." The absence of an explicit agent and the ambiguity of *man* generate the range of readings.

Al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī, and al-Qurṭubī record the principal options, and they can be arranged by what they take *man* to refer to.

**Reading One: God Himself Is Meant, Without Containment**
