The dominant reading among the classical exegetes is that *man fi'l-nār* refers to God — but not in the sense that God is spatially inside the fire. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the meaning is either that the *fire* was blessed, or that the *Light of God* manifested in the fire was blessed. The particle *fī* ("in") is understood as indicating the *place of manifestation* rather than the place of containment — as when one says "the king is in the palace," meaning his authority and presence are operative there, not that his substance fills its rooms.

Ibn Kathīr develops this reading and connects it to the parallel verse in Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ, where the same incident is described with different words:

> *"Then when he came to it, he was called from the right bank of the valley, in the blessed site (bi'l-buqʿati'l-mubārakah), from the tree: 'O Moses! Verily I am God, Lord of the worlds.'"* (Qur'an 28:30)

Notice what al-Qaṣaṣ does: it locates the blessing in the *site* — *al-buqʿah al-mubārakah*, the blessed ground — and it locates the voice as coming *from the tree*. Al-Zamakhsharī and others therefore read 27:8 in the light of 28:30 and conclude that *būrika man fi'l-nār* means "blessed is the one [who is at] the fire," or that the place of the fire is what is blessed because a great event occurred there. The two verses together resolve each other: the Qur'an is describing a location made holy by a manifestation, and a voice coming from that location.

**Reading Two: "The One Around It" — Moses and the Angels**

The second half of the sentence, *wa man ḥawlahā* — "and the one around it" — is understood by al-Ṭabarī and al-Thaʿlabī to refer to the angels, or to Moses and the angels together, all of them blessed by God. The blessing is thus pronounced over the entire scene: the place of manifestation, the angelic host present at it, and the prophet who has walked into it. This reading has the advantage of explaining why both phrases appear: the sentence is a benediction over the whole encounter, spoken at the moment the encounter begins.

**Reading Three: Man as Mā — "Blessed Is That Which Is in the Fire"**

A third reading, recorded by al-Thaʿlabī, exploits a known feature of Arabic: the particle *man*, which ordinarily means "who" or "the one who," can in some usages carry the general sense of *mā*, "what" or "that which." On this reading the sentence would mean "Blessed is that which is in the fire" — that is, the fire itself, or the light within it. The reading avoids any appearance of personal reference to God and keeps the blessing on the created phenomenon.

**Reading Four: Al-Rāzī's Preference**

Al-Rāzī, whose method is to weigh readings against context and linguistic probability, notes a further possibility: that *būrika man fi'l-nār* implies "Blessed is the one [seeking after] the fire" — Moses — "or the One [whose power and authority is] in the fire." The second formulation is the one that satisfies both the plain wording and the creed: God's *power and authority* (*qudrah* and *sulṭān*) are in the fire, in the sense that the fire does what it does only by His command, and the voice speaks from it only by His arrangement. Nothing is contained; everything is governed.

**"Blessed" as "Holy"**

Ibn Kathīr and al-Thaʿlabī record a further gloss: *būrika* is also understood to mean *muqaddas*, "holy, sanctified." On this reading the sentence is a declaration of sanctity over the scene — the fire, its surroundings, and everyone present are declared holy, set apart from the ordinary. This connects directly to the other Qur'anic descriptions of the valley:

> *"Truly I am thy Lord, so remove thy two sandals; verily thou art in the sacred valley, Ṭuwā."* (Qur'an 20:12)

> *"Go thou and thy brother with Our signs, and be not remiss in My remembrance. Go both of you unto Pharaoh; truly he has rebelled."* (Qur'an 20:42–43)

The command to remove the sandals is the ritual acknowledgment of what verse 8 announces: this ground is not like other ground.

**Was It Fire or Light?**

The commentators are nearly unanimous that what Moses saw was not fire in the ordinary sense. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said it was not fire but a flickering light, and in another report he called it "the Light of the Lord of the worlds." Al-Qurṭubī and al-Thaʿlabī note that the Arabs sometimes used *nār* in place of *nūr*, and that the phenomenon was described as fire because that is how it appeared to Moses from a distance in the dark. The same theme appears in the Qur'an's own account of the two men at the fire in Sūrah Ṭā Hā:

> *"And We made the fire a coolness and a peace for Abraham"* — where fire behaves contrary to its nature by command (cf. 21:69) — and in the description of the light of God at 24:35: *"God is the Light of the heavens and the earth; the likeness of His Light is as a niche wherein is a lamp..."*

The theological point that the commentators extract is significant and directly relevant to the creed: light and fire, in the Qur'an, are not opposed substances but graduated manifestations, and God's light can appear in forms that human perception misnames. Moses called it fire. God let him call it fire. What came out of it was a voice.

**The Ḥadīth the Commentators Cite Here**

It is precisely at this verse that the classical exegetes — Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Thaʿlabī — cite a ḥadīth of Muslim concerning the Divine veil, because it speaks directly of light and fire in relation to God:

> *"God does not sleep, nor is it fitting for Him to sleep. He lowers the balance and raises it. The deed of the night is raised to Him before the deed of the day, and the deed of the day before the deed of the night. His veil is light — [in another narration: fire] — and if He were to remove it, the splendors of His Face would burn up everything of His creation upon which His gaze fell."* (Muslim 179, from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

The relevance to verse 8 is exact. The ḥadīth identifies light or fire as the *veil* of God — not His substance, not His location, but the covering through which His manifestation is moderated to what creatures can bear. Moses saw a fire because that is what a creature can bear of the veil; had the veil lifted, nothing would have remained to see it. The commentators cite the report here to establish that the fire at Ṭuwā belongs to this category of manifested light, and to warn the reader against reading the verse as a statement of location.

**"And Glory Be to God, Lord of the Worlds"**

The verse's second sentence — *wa subḥāna Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn* — is a declaration of transcendence placed immediately after the difficult phrase. *Subḥāna Llāh* is from the root *s-b-ḥ*, to swim, to glide, to be far removed; the verbal noun *tasbīḥ* means to declare God far removed from every deficiency and from every likeness to created things. Al-Rāzī and al-Bayḍāwī observe the function of the placement: whatever the reader has just understood from "blessed is the One in the fire," the very next words remove from God every implication of containment, location, or resemblance. The verse builds in its own safeguard.

This is a general principle of Qur'anic discourse that Ibn Taymiyyah formulated at length in his treatises on the Divine Attributes: the Qur'an describes God in terms drawn from creation, and immediately pairs those terms with declarations of transcendence, so that neither affirmation nor negation is left alone. Affirmation without negation produces anthropomorphism (*tashbīh*); negation without affirmation produces emptiness (*taʿṭīl*). The Qur'an's method — and verse 8 is a textbook instance — is to affirm and to transcend in a single breath: blessed is the One in the fire, *and glory be to God, Lord of the worlds.*

**Why the Scene Begins With a Blessing**

There is a last observation worth making about the emotional register of this verse. Moses arrives exhausted, cold, and afraid, having lost the road with a family depending on him. The first thing said to him is not a command, not a rebuke, not a warning. It is a benediction: *blessed is...* over him and over everything around him. The commission that follows — go to Pharaoh, the most dangerous assignment in prophetic history — is preceded by a blessing.

The order is not accidental and it is not unique. The Qur'an's first revelation to Muhammad ﷺ begins with *iqraʾ bismi rabbik* — "Recite in the Name of thy Lord" — and every sūrah but one begins with *In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful*. The pattern is that divine address opens with mercy before it moves to demand. A reader who has ever been given a difficult task by someone who first told him that he was valued will recognize the wisdom of the sequence: the blessing is what makes the commission bearable. Moses walks into the fire looking for a torch, and walks out with the hardest job in the world — but he walks out already blessed.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:9

> **O Moses! Verily, it is I, God, the Mighty, the Wise!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Self-Identification: A Name Before a Command**

The call that began with a blessing now identifies itself. *Yā Mūsā innahu anā Llāhu'l-ʿAzīzu'l-Ḥakīm* — "O Moses! Verily, it is I, God, the Mighty, the Wise." The speaker names Himself before He gives His instruction, and the naming is not a formality. A person who is addressed from a fire in the middle of a desert at night has one question that must be answered before any other can be asked: *who is speaking?* If the answer is a jinn, everything that follows is a deception. If the answer is God, everything that follows is a commission. The Qur'an's account places the identification first and puts it in the first person — *anā Allāh*, "I am God."

**The Grammatical Ambiguity, and What Each Reading Yields**

Al-Ṭabarī records that the sentence can be read in more than one way, and the readings differ by a single vowel. The Arabic written form allows both *annahu anā Allāh* and *innahu anā Allāh*.

On the first reading, *anna* is the particle of substantiation introducing a clause: "Verily it is [the case] that I am God" — that is, the reality is that I am God; be certain of it. On the second, *inna* functions as an emphatic introduction to a new statement: "Truly, I am God" — or, as al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī render it, simply "O Moses! I am God."

The difference between the two is small in English and larger in effect. The first reading makes the sentence an assertion about *what is the case* — a statement of reality addressed to Moses' uncertainty. The second makes it a direct self-presentation — God introducing Himself. Al-Ṭabarī prefers the second because the context is one of first address; al-Rāzī notes both are grammatically sound and that the second is more natural in a call (*nidāʾ*) that has just named its addressee. Either way, the substance is identical: the speaker is God, and He says so in His own Name.

**Anā Allāh and the Hebrew Ehyeh Asher Ehyeh**

The source commentary makes a comparative observation that deserves careful statement, because it clarifies what this verse is *not* doing. In Exodus 3:14, when Moses asks God for His name, the Hebrew answer is *ehyeh asher ehyeh*, rendered variously as "I am that I am," "I am who I am," "I will be what I will be," or "He who is." That phrase has generated two thousand years of argument in Jewish and Christian theology — about whether it is a name or a refusal of a name, about whether it asserts being or becoming, about whether God is disclosing or withholding.

The Islamic account of the same encounter is different in structure. God here identifies Himself by His *Name* — *Allāh* — and then qualifies that Name with two attributes: *al-ʿAzīz*, the Mighty, and *al-Ḥakīm*, the Wise. There is no philosophical puzzle at the surface of the sentence. The Qur'an also gives, in Sūrah Ṭā Hā, a version that is closer in form to the Exodus account but still names rather than evades:

> *"Verily I am I, God; there is no god but I. So worship Me, and establish the prayer for My remembrance."* (Qur'an 20:14)

The commentators note that the currents of Jewish and Christian interpretation which hold *ehyeh asher ehyeh* to be, or to contain, a Name of God overlap with the Islamic understanding. What the Qur'an does not do is leave the identification open-ended. The Name is given, the oneness is asserted, and the two attributes are attached — so that Moses knows not only *who* is speaking but *what kind of power* is speaking.

**Al-ʿAzīz: The Mighty, the Unreachable, the Victorious**

*Al-ʿAzīz* comes from the root *ʿ-z-z*, whose basic sense is hardness, rarity, and strength: *ʿizz* is might and honor; *ʿazīz* is something precious because scarce; *aʿazz* is dearer; and *ʿazz* also means to be unattainable. Al-Rāghib's lexicon gives three components to the word: *ʿizzat al-ghalabah*, the might of overcoming; *ʿizzat al-ḥājah*, the preciousness of something rare and needed; and *ʿizzat al-shiddah*, hardness and resistance. When applied to God, *al-ʿAzīz* means the One who cannot be overcome, who is beyond reach, and who is without equal — the Qur'an's own gloss is:

> *"Say: 'He is God, the One, God the Everlasting Refuge. He begets not, nor is He begotten, and there is none equal (kufuʾan) unto Him.'"* (Qur'an 112:1–4)

The choice of this Name at this moment is exact. Moses is about to be sent to Pharaoh — a man who claimed divinity himself, who commanded the largest military and administrative apparatus on earth, and who had raised Moses in his own palace. The first thing Moses needs to know about the One sending him is that this One cannot be beaten. *Al-ʿAzīz* is the Name that answers the fear of a prophet walking into a tyrant's court. The Qur'an will make the same pairing at the climax of the confrontation, when the magicians fall prostrate:

> *"They said: 'No harm! Truly unto our Lord we shall return.'"* (Qur'an 26:50)

and in the refrain of al-Shuʿarāʾ, repeated eight times: *"And verily thy Lord, He is the Mighty (al-ʿAzīz), the Merciful (al-Raḥīm)."* In that sūrah the Name is joined to mercy; here it is joined to wisdom. Both pairings make the same point from different sides: this might is not brute force.

**Al-Ḥakīm: Wisdom as the Manner of the Might**

*Al-Ḥakīm*, as noted at verse 6, is from *ḥ-k-m* — to restrain, to decide, to place each thing where it belongs. God's being *Ḥakīm* means that nothing He does is arbitrary, wasteful, or misplaced: every act has an end fitting to it, and every command serves a wisdom. This Name appears paired with *al-ʿAzīz* more than twenty times in the Qur'an, and the pairing is the creed's answer to two opposite errors. Might without wisdom is tyranny; wisdom without might is ineffectual goodwill. God is named as both, in that order, in the first sentence He speaks to Moses at the fire.

For a prophet receiving an impossible assignment, the pairing has a specific consolation. Moses will do things that look inexplicable — throw down his staff, put his hand in his bosom, go unarmed to an army, ask for the release of a nation of slaves. He will be told he is a murderer, a liar, and a sorcerer. The Name *al-Ḥakīm* tells him in advance that there is a design he is not being shown in full. This is the same posture the sūrah will require of its reader at verse 88 — *"the Work of God, Who perfects all things"* — and at verse 50, where the conspirators of Thamūd are told that *"they devised a plot, and We devised a plot, while they were not aware."* God's wisdom is often invisible to the people inside the story while they are living it.

**The First Person and the Second Person: How God Speaks to a Prophet**

There is a detail in the sentence worth pausing on. God begins with *yā Mūsā* — "O Moses" — using the personal name, without a title, without "My servant," without "O prophet." The intimacy of a bare name in a divine address is a feature of the Qur'an's portrayal of prophecy throughout: *"O Adam, dwell thou and thy wife in the Garden"* (2:35); *"O Noah, descend in peace from Us"* (11:48); *"O Abraham, turn away from this"* (11:76); *"O Jesus son of Mary, remember My blessing upon thee"* (5:110); *"O Yahya, hold the Book firmly"* (19:12).

In every case the Name of God follows or precedes the name of the man, and the effect is a conversation rather than a broadcast. This is why the Sufi tradition, as recorded at verse 6, insisted that the Qur'an must be heard *min Allāh* — from God. The register of prophetic address is personal, and the reader who recites the Qur'an is being addressed in that same register: the commands are second-person, the promises are second-person, and the one Name the Qur'an never withholds from a servant who turns to Him is his own.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:10

> **And, "Cast thy staff!" Then when he saw it quivering like a serpent, he turned in retreat without returning. "O Moses! Fear not! Verily the messengers fear not in My Presence,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Command, and the First Miracle**

The commission begins not with a sermon but with an instruction about an object Moses is already holding: *wa an alqi ʿaṣāka* — "and cast thy staff." The staff is the ordinary tool of a shepherd: the thing he leans on when walking, uses to beat down leaves for his flock, and carries as a defense. The Qur'an elsewhere lets Moses describe it himself, in one of the most humanly charming exchanges in the Book:

> *"And what is that in thy right hand, O Moses? He said: 'It is my staff. I lean upon it, and I beat down [leaves] with it for my flock, and I have other uses for it.'"* (Qur'an 20:17–18)

The command to throw it is therefore a command to surrender the one thing he relies on. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr note the sequence's logic: before God can give Moses a sign, Moses must let go of his support. The miracle does not replace the staff; it transforms what the staff becomes when it is thrown down in obedience.

**Tahtazzu: The Verb of Trembling Motion**

When Moses throws it, the Qur'an says: *fa-lammā raʾāhā tahtazzu ka-annahā jānn* — "then when he saw it quivering, as though it were a *jānn*." The verb *tahtazzu* is Form VIII from *h-z-z*, to shake, to agitate, to vibrate rapidly. Arabic uses the same root for the shaking of a palm trunk (*huzzī ilayki bi-jidhʿi'l-nakhlah*, 19:25), for the trembling of the earth in an earthquake (*idhā zulzilati'l-arḍu zilzālahā*, cognate in sense), and for rapid oscillating movement generally. The word chosen is not "moving" or "crawling" but *vibrating* — the particular quick lateral motion of a snake.

*Jānn* is one of the most carefully chosen words in the verse. It has two related meanings in Arabic and both are operative. Lexically, *jānn* is a small, swift, intensely active serpent — not the great python (*thuʿbān*) but the quick, darting kind. It is also, in the Qur'an, a word for a class of jinn:

> *"And the jinn (al-jānn) We created before, from the fire of the scorching wind."* (Qur'an 15:27)

> *"And He created the jinn (al-jānn) from a smokeless flame of fire."* (Qur'an 55:15)

The double meaning is not a coincidence of vocabulary; it is how the Qur'an describes the miracle. In Sūrah al-Aʿrāf the same staff becomes *thuʿbān mubīn*, "a manifest serpent" (7:107), and in al-Shuʿarāʾ it becomes a *thuʿbān* seen by all the assembly (26:32). Al-Naml alone uses *jānn*, and the commentators explain the choice: the emphasis here is on the *speed and agitation* of the movement — it moved so fast that its motion was like the darting of the swiftest kind of snake — while elsewhere the emphasis is on its *size*. Ibn Kathīr reconciles the accounts by noting that the transformation was real and complete, and that each sūrah highlights a different aspect of what appeared.

**"He Turned in Retreat Without Returning": Fear as Evidence of Reality**

*Wallā mudbiran wa lam yuʿaqqib* — "he turned his back, fleeing, and did not look back." The verb *yuʿaqqib* is from *ʿ-q-b*, heel, to follow on the heels, to turn back; *lam yuʿaqqib* means he did not turn around, did not return, did not even glance behind him. Al-Qurṭubī records the interpretation that it means he did not look to either side — so total was the flight that he did not venture a sideways glance.

This detail has been much discussed, because it raises an obvious question: why does a prophet, who has just been addressed by God and told that he is in the presence of the Mighty and the Wise, run away from a snake? The commentators give three answers, all of them illuminating.

The first is natural and unembarrassing: fear of a sudden serpent is a reflex, not a judgment. Ibn Kathīr notes that Moses' flight was the involuntary reaction of a human being to something terrifying appearing where a stick had been a moment before, and that the Qur'an reports it without censure precisely because it is not a sin. The Qur'an's prophets are portrayed as fully human — Adam forgets, Jonah despairs, Jacob grieves until his eyes whiten, Muhammad ﷺ trembles after the first revelation and asks to be covered — and the honesty of these portraits is part of the Book's claim to truthfulness.

The second answer is evidential: the fact that Moses ran is proof that the thing was real. A conjurer who produces an illusion is not frightened by it. Al-Rāzī makes this point in his discussion of the miracle's function: the first witness to the staff's transformation was the man holding it, and his terror is the testimony of a witness who had no motive to exaggerate.

The third answer is theological, and it prepares for the next verse. Moses fled because he had reason to feel unsafe in the presence of God. He had killed a man (28:15), he was a fugitive from Pharaoh's court, and he stood now in a holy valley hearing the voice of the Almighty. His flight is not only from the serpent; it is the flight of a man who knows he has a case against him. That is why the reassurance that follows is not only "do not fear the snake" but "the messengers do not fear in My presence" — and then, immediately, the exception.

**"Fear Not": The Consolation Given to Every Prophet**

*Lā takhaf* — "fear not." The Qur'an repeats this exact address to its prophets at moments of maximum danger, and the repetition is a pattern worth collecting:

> *"We said: 'Fear not; verily thou art the one who prevails.'"* (Qur'an 20:68 — to Moses before the magicians)

> *"Fear not, We are with you both, hearing and seeing."* (Qur'an 20:46 — to Moses and Aaron, sent to Pharaoh)

> *"O Abraham! Fear not, for I am not afraid of you"* — cf. the angels' words to Abraham (11:70) and to Lot (11:70, 15:53)

> *"Fear not and grieve not, and receive the glad tidings of the Garden that you were promised."* (Qur'an 41:30 — to the believers at death)

> *"When God is your helper, who can overcome you?"* — and *"Fear not; verily, we are with you both"* — cf. 3:139, 9:40

And in the most intimate form, addressed to Muhammad ﷺ in the cave while his pursuers stood outside:

> *"If you help him not — God already helped him when those who disbelieve drove him out, the second of two, when they were in the cave, and he said to his companion: 'Grieve not; verily God is with us.'"* (Qur'an 9:40)

The reassurance given to Moses here is thus not a private comfort but an instance of a divine habit. God's method with His messengers is to place a prohibition on fear at the exact point where fear is most reasonable. The reason is practical as well as spiritual: a frightened man cannot deliver a message to a tyrant. Courage is not the absence of the reflex — Moses ran — but the acceptance of the command that follows the reflex.

**"Verily the Messengers Fear Not in My Presence": The Rule**

*Inna lā yakhāfu ladayya'l-mursalūn* — "Truly the messengers do not fear in My Presence." The word *ladayya* — "with Me, in My presence, at My side" — is the same family of prepositions as *ladun* in verse 6, indicating nearness rather than location. The sentence states a general rule about prophets, not a comment on Moses: those who are sent do not fear when they stand before God.

Al-Ṭabarī explains the rule in terms of security: a messenger knows that the One who sent him protects him, and that the message will reach its destination whether or not he survives delivering it. Fear is a response to uncertain danger; a messenger's danger is certain but its outcome is not in his hands, and the certainty of protection removes the ground of fear. The Prophet ﷺ described the same state in a report recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in the context of the night journey and of his own composure before danger:

> *"The most beloved of deeds to God are those done consistently, even if small."* — and on fear specifically, the ḥadīth of the trench and of Ḥunayn show him standing where others fled (al-Bukhārī 2977; Muslim 1776).

More directly relevant is the Prophetic statement recorded by Aḥmad and al-Tirmidhī concerning the reliance that removes fear:

> *"If you were to rely upon God with the reliance He deserves, He would provide for you as He provides for the birds: they go out in the morning hungry and return in the evening full."* (al-Tirmidhī 2344, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The messengers' freedom from fear in God's presence is the same state, perfected. And the exception in the next verse will show what happens when a prophet's fear is not about God's danger but about his own record.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:11

> **save one who did wrong, then replaced evil with good. For truly I am Forgiving, Merciful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Generated a Century of Theology**

*Illā man ẓalama thumma baddala ḥusnan baʿda sūʾin fa-innī ghafūrun raḥīm* — "save one who did wrong, then replaced evil with good. For truly I am Forgiving, Merciful." This single clause is among the most contested in the Qur'an's prophetic narratives, because it appears to attach an exception to a rule about messengers — and the doctrine of prophetic infallibility (*ʿiṣmah*) is held by the vast majority of Muslim theologians to preclude prophets from sin, at least after their investiture.

The readings offered by the commentators divide into three families, and each family reflects a different grammatical understanding of *illā* — "except, save."

**Reading One: The Exception Refers to Moses Himself, Before His Prophethood**

Al-Ṭabarī holds that *man ẓalama* is an oblique reference to Moses, who had struck and killed an Egyptian who was fighting with an Israelite:

> *"And he entered the city at a time of its inattention, and found therein two men fighting, one of his own party and one of his enemies. And he who was of his party called for help against him who was of his enemies, and Moses struck him with his fist and killed him. He said: 'This is of the work of Satan. Truly he is an enemy, manifestly misleading.'"* (Qur'an 28:15)

On this reading, Moses' fear in the previous verse was not only fear of the serpent but fear that the killing would stand between him and God; and the exception is God's way of saying, in effect: the messengers do not fear in My presence — and neither does the one who did wrong and then changed it. Moses had wronged; Moses had repented — *"My Lord! Truly I have wronged myself, so forgive me," and He forgave him* (28:16) — and therefore Moses falls within the reassurance rather than outside it.

**Reading Two: Illā as "Nor" — A Conjunction, Not an Exception**

Some commentators, recorded by al-Ṭabarī and al-Thaʿlabī, understood *illā* here not as "except" but in one of its other attested senses, so that the sentence reads: *"Verily the messengers fear not in My Presence, nor does one who did wrong and then replaced evil with good."* On this reading there is no exception at all: the verse extends the security to a *second* class — repentant wrongdoers — alongside the messengers. The reassurance broadens rather than narrows.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that those who sought alternate meanings for *illā* did so out of reluctance to attribute sin to prophets, since their protection from sin is an established doctrine among the vast majority of Muslim theologians. But he also makes the counter-observation that has kept the discussion honest: many scholars accept that prophets could commit errors (*dhanb*) *before* their investiture with prophethood; and moreover, it is a sign of sincerity that a person fears his repentance may be incomplete. A saying attributed to al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, recorded by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, states the position bluntly:

> *"Prophets sin and are chastised."*

**Reading Three: The General Case — Any Wrongdoer Who Repents**

A third reading, favored by al-Rāzī and by many later commentators, takes the clause as general rather than as referring to Moses at all. On this view the verse establishes a principle for everyone: fear in God's presence is removed by two things — being His messenger, or having wronged and then replaced evil with good. The second category is open to all human beings, and it is the category into which the entire readership of the Qur'an falls.

This reading makes the verse one of the most hopeful in the chapter. The ordinary believer is not a messenger and cannot claim the messenger's security. But the verse tells him that there is a second door into the same security, and that door is repentance. *Thumma baddala ḥusnan baʿda sūʾin* — "then he replaced good after evil": not merely regretted, not merely apologized, but *replaced* — substituted an act for an act, a direction for a direction. Al-Ṭabarī glosses this explicitly as repentance (*tawbah*).

**Baddala Ḥusnan Baʿda Sūʾin: Replacement, Not Erasure**

The verb *baddala* — Form II from *b-d-l*, to exchange, to substitute, to change one thing for another — is precise, and its precision matters. The verse does not say "then he regretted" or "then evil was removed from him." It says he *exchanged*: evil went out and good came in. The picture is of a substitution in which the sinner himself performs the second half. God forgives; the servant replaces.

This is the Qur'an's consistent account of repentance, and it explains why the tradition distinguished mere remorse from *tawbah*. The three classical conditions — ceasing the sin, regretting it, and resolving never to return, with the addition of restoring rights to their owners where the sin involved another person's property or honor — are all contained in the single verb *baddala*. Remorse alone leaves the ledger as it was; replacement writes a new entry over the old.

The Qur'an makes the same promise in the most explicit terms elsewhere:

> *"Save those who repent and believe and work righteousness — for them God will replace their evil deeds with good deeds; and God is ever Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 25:70)

> *"And verily I am Forgiving toward him who repents, believes, works righteousness, and then is guided."* (Qur'an 20:82)

Verse 20:82 is especially relevant because it appears in the *same narrative* — Sūrah Ṭā Hā's account of Moses at the fire — and it adds a fourth element, "and then is guided," which al-Rāzī explains as steadfastness after repentance: not merely turning once but staying turned.

**"For Truly I Am Forgiving, Merciful": The Two Names That Close the Clause**

The verse ends with *fa-innī ghafūrun raḥīm*. The *fāʾ* ("so, then, for") links the Names directly to the exception: *because* there is one who wronged and then replaced, *therefore* I am Forgiving, Merciful. The Names are the ground of the possibility, not its result.

*Ghafūr* is from *gh-f-r*, to cover, to conceal, to protect by covering — the same root as *mighfar*, the helmet that covers the head in battle. God's forgiveness is a *covering*: the sin is not merely cancelled but veiled, so that it does not disgrace the servant in this world or in the next. *Raḥīm* is from *r-ḥ-m*, the womb — mercy conceived as the protective, nourishing, unearned tenderness of a mother for what is in her womb. The two Names together describe the two halves of what a repentant person needs: the past covered, and the future sustained.

The pairing *ghafūr raḥīm* appears more than seventy times in the Qur'an, most often exactly here — at the end of a statement about sin. It is the Book's habitual cadence: the mention of wrongdoing is followed by the mention of the Names, so that no reader can arrive at the subject of his own sins without arriving at the same breath in the subject of God's covering. And in the case of verse 11, the placement is doing something specific: it answers the fear that verse 10 described. Moses ran. God's response to the running is not a rebuke but a statement of His own forgiveness.

**Why the Verse Is Placed Here, in a Story About a Snake**

A final observation on placement. The immediate occasion of the fear is a serpent — a physical danger. The immediate reassurance is not about serpents; it is about wrongdoing and forgiveness. The Qur'an is telling the reader what the real content of Moses' fear was. And in doing so it makes the verse about every reader. The thing that makes a human being unable to stand in God's presence is not danger from outside; it is the record inside. And the removal of that fear is available on terms stated in twelve words: whoever did wrong and then replaced evil with good — *I am Forgiving, Merciful.*

The doctrine of *ʿiṣmah* is not threatened by this verse on any of the three readings. What the verse establishes is not that prophets sin, but that the door through which a sinner enters God's security is the same door that a prophet stands inside — repentance, replacement, and the two Names. That is why al-Qurṭubī could note, with characteristic balance, both that the majority uphold prophetic protection from sin *and* that it is a mark of sincerity for a person to fear that his repentance is incomplete. The believer who has repented and still fears is not lacking faith; he is standing where Moses stood, in the holy valley, having just been told not to be afraid.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:12

> **And insert thy hand in thy bosom; it will come forth white, without blemish—among nine signs to Pharaoh and his people. Truly they have been an iniquitous people."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Sign, Given in Private Before It Is Used in Public**

*Wa adkhil yadaka fī jaybika takhruj bayḍāʾa min ghayri sūʾ* — "And insert thy hand into thy bosom; it will come forth white, without blemish." The word *jayb* is the opening of a garment at the neck and chest — the bosom, the collar. The instruction is to put the hand inside the shirt against the chest and draw it out. What comes out is *bayḍāʾ* — white — *min ghayri sūʾ*, "without evil," that is, without disease, without blemish, without the disfigurement of leprosy.

The qualification matters and the commentators are careful about it. A hand that turns white could be read as a hand afflicted — leprosy whitens the skin, and the disease was well known and greatly feared. The phrase *min ghayri sūʾ* removes that reading: the whiteness is luminous, not pathological. The same qualification appears in the parallel account:

> *"And press thy hand to thy side; it will come forth white, without blemish — another sign, that We may show thee some of Our greatest signs."* (Qur'an 20:22–23)

> *"And bring forth thy hand from thy bosom white without blemish, among the nine signs unto Pharaoh and his people."* (cf. Qur'an 27:12)

The miracle is mentioned four times in the Qur'an — at 7:108, 20:22, 26:33, and 28:32 — and each mention places it in a different position in the narrative. Here in al-Naml it is given *before* Moses goes to Pharaoh, as a private demonstration; in al-Shuʿarāʾ (26:33) it is produced before the assembled court. Al-Naml's placement is thus didactic: the sign is shown to the prophet first, in the dark valley, with no audience, so that he will know what he is carrying before he is required to display it.

This is a principle of the Qur'an's account of preparation generally. God does not send a man into a confrontation without first letting him see, alone, what God will do through him. The Prophet ﷺ's own preparation followed the same shape: the first revelation came in seclusion, in a cave, with no witnesses, and Khadījah's confirmation was private before the public proclamation began three years later. The private demonstration is what makes the public one possible, because the man who has seen the sign once does not need to see it again in order to trust it.

**"Among Nine Signs": The Number and Its Source**

The verse names the total: *fī tisʿi āyātin ilā Firʿawna wa qawmih* — "among nine signs, to Pharaoh and his people." The Qur'an refers to the same number elsewhere and specifies that they were clear:

> *"And verily We gave Moses nine clear signs. So ask the Children of Israel: when he came unto them, Pharaoh said unto him: 'Verily I think thee, O Moses, one bewitched.'"* (Qur'an 17:101)

The commentators enumerate the nine differently, but the standard list — given by al-Ṭabarī from Ibn ʿAbbās and by Ibn Kathīr — comprises: the staff, the white hand, the years of drought and shortage of crops, the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood, and the parting of the sea. Some count the splitting of the sea among the nine and omit another; some include the raising of the mountain over the Children of Israel. The enumeration at 7:130–133 gives the sequence of plagues explicitly:

> *"And We seized Pharaoh's people with years of drought and shortage of crops, that haply they might take heed... So We sent upon them the flood and the locusts and the lice and the frogs and the blood, distinct signs, but they waxed proud and were a sinful people."* (Qur'an 7:130, 133)

The number nine is not arbitrary numerology; it functions as a statement of *sufficiency*. Nine distinct, verifiable, public signs is far more evidence than any reasonable person requires, and the Qur'an's point in naming the number is that the rejection to follow was not caused by insufficient proof. Verse 13 will make this explicit, and verse 14 will complete it: they rejected the signs *though their souls were convinced of them.*

**"Truly They Have Been an Iniquitous People": A Perfect Tense About a Present State**

The verse closes with *innahum kānū qawman fāsiqīn* — "truly they have been an iniquitous people." The word *fāsiq* is from *f-s-q*, whose literal sense is to exit, to come out of, to leave a container — a date that *fasaqa* is one that has popped out of its skin. In religious usage the *fāsiq* is one who has come out of obedience, who has exited the boundary. Al-Rāghib distinguishes *fisq* from *kufr*: *fisq* is lesser than outright disbelief in some usages and greater than ordinary disobedience in others; it denotes a settled departure from the command.

The grammatical form is significant. *Kānū* is the past tense of "to be," and Arabic uses it — as the commentators note repeatedly — to describe a *settled, continuing condition* rather than a completed past event. "They have been" here means "they are, and have long been, that kind of people." The statement is made *before* Moses arrives, in the valley, at the moment of his commissioning. God tells him the character of his audience in advance: they are not confused, they are not uninformed, they are not open. They are a people who have exited obedience and intend to stay out.

This is a mercy to Moses as much as a warning. A messenger who expects to persuade will be devastated by rejection; a messenger who has been told in advance that the rejection is a settled disposition will understand it correctly — as information about them, not as evidence against his message. The Qur'an gives Muhammad ﷺ the same preparation repeatedly:

> *"And if it is hard upon thee that they turn away from thee — then, if thou art able to seek a tunnel in the earth or a ladder in the sky, that thou mayest bring them a sign... Truly had God willed, He would have gathered them upon guidance. So be not among the ignorant."* (Qur'an 6:35)

> *"It is the same for them whether thou warnest them or warnest them not; they will not believe."* (Qur'an 36:10)

**The Spiritual Reading: The Hand in the Bosom**

The source commentary records an interpretation from the Sufi tradition (Aj, Ibn ʿAjībah) that reads the sign allegorically: one should place one's spiritual meditation into one's heart, and one's thoughts will then emerge white and gleaming, leading to greater understanding and vision and a fuller realization of the virtues necessary to journey to God.

The allegory is built on the physical facts of the sign, and it is coherent. The hand goes in at the *jayb* — the opening at the chest, closest to the heart — and comes out changed in quality, luminous, without disease. The spiritual reading takes the heart as the bosom: whatever a person puts into his heart and holds there — a thought, an intention, a repeated remembrance — will come out transformed, and if what was placed there was the remembrance of God, what comes out will be light. The Qur'an supports the general principle independently:

> *"Verily, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

> *"That Day when neither wealth nor sons avail, save him who comes unto God with a sound heart (qalb salīm)."* (Qur'an 26:88–89)

Note that the two verses are from al-Shuʿarāʾ, the sūrah immediately preceding this one, and that *qalb salīm* — a sound, unblemished heart — uses the same idea of freedom from defect as our verse's *min ghayri sūʾ*, "without blemish." The hand comes out white *without evil*; the heart arrives sound *without defect*. The two images describe the same state from two directions: what is put into the heart in private emerges as the visible quality of the person in public.

**Practical Application: The Signs a Modern Reader Carries**

The verse's structure — a private demonstration, a numbered set of signs, an audience already diagnosed — has direct application. Most people who wish to speak about God to others do so without ever having been shown anything privately. They have arguments but no experience; they have inherited conclusions but no personal verification. The order in verse 12 is the reverse: first the hand goes into the bosom in the dark valley where nobody is watching, and only then is the sign counted among the nine to be shown to Pharaoh.

The Qur'an's own instruction for producing that private verification is not a miracle but a practice: reflection upon the created order, which this very sūrah will prescribe five times in verses 59–64. A person who has actually sat with the fact that rain fell and a garden grew — and that he cannot make a single tree grow (v. 60) — has inserted his hand into his own bosom and drawn it out white. He will not need to be frightened by the Pharaoh in front of him, whoever that Pharaoh is: a boss, a parent, a peer group, a culture, or his own appetite. He will already know what he is carrying.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:13

> **And when Our signs came to them as a clear portent, they said, "This is manifest sorcery!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response to Evidence, and the Word Chosen for It**

*Falammā jāʾat-hum āyātunā mubṣiratan qālū hādhā siḥrun mubīn* — "Then when Our signs came unto them, clear and self-evident, they said: 'This is manifest sorcery.'" The verse compresses an entire epistemological event into a single sentence: signs arrive, they arrive with clarity, and the response is a *relabeling*. Nothing about the signs is disputed. They are not called false, or weak, or uncertain. They are called something else — magic.

**Mubṣiratan: The Signs That See**

The word *mubṣirah* is the most striking feature of the verse, and the commentators worked on it carefully. It is the active participle of Form IV from *b-ṣ-r*, to see — *abṣara*, to perceive clearly — and it means literally "seeing" or "that which sees." Applied to signs, which do not have eyes, the form is a deliberate Arabic idiom known as the transferred or figurative active participle: the quality of the one who perceives is attributed to the thing perceived. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, and al-Bayḍāwī all explain it on this principle: the signs are described as *seeing* because they are so luminous, so evident, and so self-disclosing that anyone who looks at them *sees* through them to the truth — as though the signs themselves did the seeing on the observer's behalf.

Arabic has the same idiom in ordinary speech: one says *laylun nāṣib*, "a tiring night," meaning a night in which one is tired; and *naḥwun shāʿir*, "a poetical verse." Al-Zamakhsharī cites this usage explicitly to justify the translation "clear" or "enlightening." The effect in English is best captured as "signs that could be seen through, signs that made one see."

The theological consequence is important. The verse says the clarity was in the signs, not in the observers. Pharaoh's people were not presented with ambiguous evidence and reached a reasonable conclusion; they were presented with evidence so clear that the word for it was "seeing," and they nonetheless produced a name for it that meant the opposite. That is why verse 14 will add the crucial clause — *they rejected them though their souls were convinced of them* — and why al-Ṭabarī can state flatly that it was arrogance that prevented them from accepting what they knew to be true in their hearts.

**Siḥrun Mubīn: The Same Adjective, Turned Around**

The response uses *mubīn* — "manifest, clear" — the very adjective with which verse 1 described the Qur'an (*kitābin mubīn*) and with which verse 12's context has been moving. The disbelievers say: *this is manifest sorcery.* They do not deny the manifestness; they relocate it. The phenomenon is obviously extraordinary — that is agreed by everyone in the story — and the only question is what kind of extraordinary it is.

This is one of the most accurate observations the Qur'an makes about the sociology of unbelief, and it repeats with almost identical wording across the prophetic narratives:

> *"And when Our clear signs came unto them, they said: 'This is manifest sorcery (siḥrun mubīn).'"* (Qur'an 28:48 — of Moses before Pharaoh's court)

> *"They said: 'O our people! Truly we have heard a Book sent down after Moses... but this is a sorcerer and a liar.'"* — cf. 46:30, 38:4

> *"And they said: 'O thou upon whom the Reminder has been sent down, verily thou art possessed.'"* (Qur'an 15:6)

> *"Even so, no messenger came unto those before them but they said: 'A sorcerer or a madman!'"* (Qur'an 51:52)

> *"Is it that they enjoin one another to this? Nay, but they are an insolent people."* (Qur'an 51:53)

Verse 51:52 is the summary statement: *every* messenger was called either a sorcerer or a madman. The two accusations divide the possibilities neatly. A man who produces extraordinary effects is either deceiving you by art (sorcerer) or is out of his mind (madman). Both labels accomplish the same purpose: they allow the hearer to accept the phenomenon while refusing its meaning.

**The Function of the Label: How Dismissal Actually Works**

It is worth being precise about the psychology here, because the verse is describing a mechanism that operates identically in every age. When a person encounters evidence that contradicts a settled commitment, he has three options: change the commitment, dispute the evidence, or *reclassify* the evidence. The third is by far the cheapest, because it requires no argument and no self-examination. It consists of assigning the phenomenon to a category that has already been discounted.

Pharaoh's courtiers could not dispute the staff — they had watched it become a serpent and had seen their own magicians fall prostrate. They could not dispute the plagues — the frogs and the blood were in their houses. But they could say "sorcery," and having said it, they did not need to do anything else. The category absorbed the anomaly. Nothing had to change.

The same operation is available to a modern reader of the Qur'an, and it is performed daily. A text of unmatched linguistic quality, produced by an illiterate merchant in a society of poets, containing accurate statements about embryology, hydrology, astronomy, and history, and predicting its own preservation — such a text can be reclassified as "a product of its time," "borrowed from earlier traditions," "the work of a genius," or "a religious document." Each of these labels does exactly what *siḥrun mubīn* did: it concedes the phenomenon and refuses the conclusion. The verse's warning is therefore not about seventh-century Egypt. It is about the human capacity to name away what one cannot answer.

**Why Sorcery Rather Than Fraud**

There is a final subtlety in the choice of accusation. Sorcery (*siḥr*) in the Qur'anic worldview is a real thing, not a stage trick — the Qur'an affirms that the magicians of Pharaoh produced genuine effects:

> *"And they threw down their ropes and their staffs, and said: 'By Pharaoh's might, we shall indeed be the victors.'"* (Qur'an 26:44)

> *"And the sorcerers fell down prostrate"* — after seeing Moses' sign exceed their art (Qur'an 20:70)

and it affirms that sorcery can harm by God's permission:

> *"And they learned from them that by which they cause separation between a man and his wife; yet they harm none thereby save by God's leave."* (Qur'an 2:102)

By calling Moses' signs sorcery, Pharaoh's people were not saying the events did not happen. They were saying the events happened through the wrong channel — through an art that human beings can acquire, rather than through a Lord whom human beings must obey. And that distinction is the whole of the matter. If the staff is a trick of the art, then Moses is a rival practitioner and can be out-practiced, bought, or executed. If the staff is a sign from God, then Moses is a messenger and must be obeyed. The accusation of sorcery is, at bottom, a refusal to obey disguised as a claim about causation.

Every age produces its own version of the same refusal. The miracle is explained as legend, the prophecy as hindsight, the moral law as cultural conditioning, the religious experience as neurochemistry. In each case the phenomenon is granted and the authority is denied — because the authority is what would have to be obeyed, and obedience is what the accusation exists to avoid. The verse names the move once, in four words, and thereby names it forever.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:14

> **They rejected them—though their souls were convinced of them—wrongfully and exultantly. So behold how the workers of corruption fared in the end!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Explains All Unbelief in the Qur'an**

*Wa jaḥadū bihā wa istayqanat-hā anfusuhum ẓulman wa ʿuluwwan* — "And they rejected them, while their souls were certain of them, wrongfully and exultantly." If verse 13 described the *form* of the rejection, verse 14 describes its *cause*, and the cause it names is not intellectual. There is not one word in this verse about doubt, insufficient evidence, alternative explanation, or honest disagreement. The verb used for what happened inside them is *istayqanat* — Form X from *y-q-n*, the same root as the *yaqīn* (certainty) that verse 3 named as the mark of the believers. Form X in Arabic conveys seeking, attaining, or coming into a state; *istayqanat-hā anfusuhum* means "their souls attained certainty of them," "their selves were convinced of them."

So the verse states the plainest and most uncomfortable diagnosis in the Qur'an: their souls were certain, and they rejected anyway. The rejection was not a failure of knowledge; it was a refusal of obedience by people who knew. Al-Ṭabarī states it in one line: it was the arrogance of Pharaoh and his people that prevented them from accepting what they knew to be true in their hearts.

**Ẓulman wa ʿUluwwan: The Two Adverbs That Name the Motive**

The verse gives two motives, both in the accusative as adverbial qualifications of the rejection.

*Ẓulman* — "wrongfully, unjustly, oppressively" — from *ẓ-l-m*, whose root sense is to place a thing in other than its proper place. A *ẓālim* is not merely cruel; he is a person who has mislocated something — rights, property, truth, himself. Al-Rāghib distinguishes three kinds of *ẓulm* in Qur'anic usage: wronging God by associating partners with Him, wronging others, and wronging oneself. All three are present in Pharaoh's case, and the Qur'an records the first in Pharaoh's own mouth at the moment of his drowning:

> *"Till, when the drowning overtook him, he said: 'I believe that there is no god save Him in Whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am among those who submit.'"* (Qur'an 10:90)

*ʿUluwwan* — "exultantly, haughtily, in self-elevation" — from *ʿ-l-w*, height, to rise, to be above. The word describes the posture of a person who has placed himself high and intends to stay there. It is the same root used of Pharaoh's claim to divinity and of the Qur'an's description of his character:

> *"Truly Pharaoh exalted himself (ʿalā) in the land and made its people into factions, oppressing a party among them, slaughtering their sons and sparing their women. Truly he was among the workers of corruption."* (Qur'an 28:4)

> *"Go unto Pharaoh; truly he has rebelled (ṭaghā)."* (Qur'an 20:24, 79:17)

The pairing of the two adverbs is exact and non-redundant. *Ẓulm* is the *content* of the wrong — a misplacement, a violation of what belongs where. *ʿUluww* is the *motive* — the desire to remain above. Together they describe the psychology of the powerful disbeliever: he does not reject the truth because he finds it unconvincing; he rejects it because accepting it would require him to be *below* something — below a former slave, below a God, below a command. And he has already wronged people; acceptance would mean accounting.

**Why Certainty Does Not Produce Submission**

This verse is the Qur'an's central answer to a question every sincere seeker asks: if the evidence is so clear, why does anyone disbelieve? The answer is not that the evidence is unclear. It is that clarity is not the operative variable. The verse demonstrates the point with a case in which certainty is *explicitly affirmed* — their souls attained it — and rejection *explicitly follows*. Between the two stands only *ẓulman wa ʿuluwwan*.

Al-Rāzī draws the general lesson at length: knowledge of the truth and acceptance of the truth are two different faculties, and the second can be blocked by the will without the first being impaired at all. The Qur'an says the same of the People of the Book in another context:

> *"Those unto whom We gave the Book recognize it as they recognize their own sons; yet verily a party among them conceal the truth while they know it."* (Qur'an 2:146)

> *"And when there came unto them a Book from God, confirming what was with them — and they had before prayed for victory against those who disbelieved — yet when there came unto them what they recognized, they disbelieved in it. So the curse of God is upon the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 2:89)

The phrase "they recognized it as they recognize their own sons" is the Qur'an's strongest possible statement of certainty — the recognition of one's own child is the paradigm of knowledge that admits no doubt — and it is paired, in the very same verse, with concealment. Certainty and rejection can coexist. That is the fact verse 14 exists to establish.

**The Psychological Cost of Knowing and Refusing**

There is a further consequence the Qur'an describes elsewhere and that follows from verse 14's diagnosis. A person who knows the truth and refuses it does not remain where he was. The refusal costs him something, and the cost is progressive. Verse 4 of this same sūrah described the mechanism: *We have made their deeds seem fair unto them, while they wander confused.* The adornment comes after the choice. And the Qur'an describes the endpoint of that process in terms of sealing:

> *"God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering."* (Qur'an 2:7)

> *"Is it the same to them whether thou warnest them or warnest them not? They will not believe. Verily We have set upon their hearts coverings, lest they understand it, and in their ears a heaviness."* (Qur'an 6:25)

The sequence is: knowledge → refusal → adornment of the refusal → sealing. Each step is a consequence of the one before, and the first step is the only one that is free. This is why the Qur'an treats the *first* refusal so seriously and why it urges immediate response to a truth once recognized. The window is not closed by God's arbitrary decision; it is closed by the accumulated weight of the person's own prior answers.

**"So Behold How the Workers of Corruption Fared in the End"**

The verse closes with *fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu'l-mufsidīn* — "then look how was the end of the workers of corruption." The imperative *fa-nẓur* — "look, behold, consider" — turns the historical report into an instruction for the reader. The Qur'an does not merely narrate the past; it directs the eyes of the present toward it.

The word *ʿāqibah* — end, outcome, consequence — comes from *ʿ-q-b*, heel, to follow on the heels: the *ʿāqibah* is what comes at the heels of a thing, its final result. And *mufsidūn* is the active participle of Form IV from *f-s-d*, to spoil, corrupt, ruin — the opposite of *ṣāliḥ*, sound and wholesome, and of *iṣlāḥ*, setting aright. The Qur'an sets this pair against each other constantly:

> *"And do not work corruption in the earth after its rectification (iṣlāḥ)."* (Qur'an 7:56)

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'Do not work corruption in the earth,' they say: 'We are only rectifiers (muṣliḥūn).'"* (Qur'an 2:11)

> *"And seek not corruption in the land; truly God loves not the workers of corruption."* (Qur'an 28:77)

Al-Naml will return to exactly this vocabulary at verse 48, describing the nine conspirators of Thamūd as men *"working corruption in the land and not setting matters aright"* — *yufsidūna fī'l-arḍ wa lā yuṣliḥūn*. The two passages are deliberately parallel: Pharaoh's court and Thamūd's conspirators are described with the same words, because they performed the same act. And the refrain *"behold how was the end"* appears throughout the Qur'an as a standing invitation to examine the record:

> *"Have they not journeyed in the land and beheld how was the end of those before them?"* (Qur'an 30:9, 42; 35:44; 40:21, 82; 47:10; 12:109)

The refrain's function is evidential. The Qur'an is asking its hearers to do something empirical: go and look at the ruins. The destroyed peoples are not arguments; they are *sites*, and the reader is instructed to visit them. This is why the verse is in the imperative and not the indicative. A statement can be disagreed with; a ruin cannot.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:15

> **And indeed We gave unto David and Solomon knowledge. And they said, "Praise be to God, Who has favored us above many of His believing servants."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pivot of the Sūrah: From Moses to the Kings Who Knew**

With verse 15 the sūrah turns from Moses and Pharaoh to David and Solomon, and the transition is not a change of subject but a change of *case*. The Mosaic narrative (vv. 7–14) has just demonstrated how power responds to knowledge: Pharaoh, possessing the greatest power on earth, was certain of the signs and rejected them out of exultation. The Solomonic narrative is about to demonstrate the opposite: a man possessing power greater than Pharaoh's in one respect — command over jinn and birds — receives knowledge and responds with praise. The two accounts are the sūrah's paired case-studies in the same question, and verse 15 opens the second with the single word that will govern everything that follows: *ʿilm*, knowledge.

*Wa laqad ātaynā Dāwūda wa Sulaymāna ʿilman* — "And verily We gave unto David and Solomon knowledge." The *lām* of emphasis in *laqad* makes this an assertion against possible denial: "and We certainly gave." The object given is not kingdom, not wealth, not victory — though all three will appear. It is knowledge. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note that the verse is thereby establishing the eminence of knowledge among God's gifts: what God gave these two men first, and what the verse mentions first, is what they knew.

**What Kind of Knowledge Was Given**

The commentators identify three overlapping referents for the knowledge in this verse.

The first is the wisdom and understanding proper to prophethood itself — for both David and Solomon were prophets in Islamic belief, not merely kings, and this is a point on which the Qur'an differs sharply from the Biblical portrait. The Qur'an speaks of David as receiving the Psalms (*Zabūr*, 4:163; 17:55) and of Solomon as a prophet given judgment. Al-Qurṭubī holds that the knowledge here is this prophetic understanding.

The second is the specific favors detailed in the verses that follow: the language of the birds (v. 16), the hosts of jinn and men (v. 17), the transport of the throne (v. 40). Al-Ṭabarī reads the knowledge of verse 15 as including these.

The third is a knowledge mentioned elsewhere in the Qur'an and attributed to David specifically:

> *"And We taught him the making of garments for you, to protect you from your own violence. Will you then be thankful?"* (Qur'an 21:80)

> *"And David and Solomon, when they gave judgment concerning the tillage, when the sheep of the people strayed therein by night — and We were witnesses to their judgment. And We gave understanding of it unto Solomon; and unto each We gave judgment and knowledge."* (Qur'an 21:78–79)

The last of these is decisive for understanding verse 15. In 21:79 God states that He gave *understanding of the case* to Solomon specifically, while giving *judgment and knowledge* to both. Solomon's distinguishing gift was not more information than his father's but a different quality of insight into particular cases — the capacity to see what should be done *here*. Al-Naml's verse 15 gives the knowledge to both men jointly, and verse 16 then shows Solomon's particular share.

**"And They Said": Two Prophets Speaking in One Voice**

*Wa qālā al-ḥamdu li'Llāhi'lladhī faḍḍalanā ʿalā kathīrin min ʿibādihi'l-muʾminīn* — "And they said: 'Praise be to God, Who has favored us above many of His believing servants.'" The verb is dual — *qālā*, "the two of them said" — and this grammatical detail carries substantial weight in the commentators' reading.

Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī observe that the dual form tells us the response to the gift was *shared and immediate*: father and son, both prophets, both given knowledge, and both arriving at the same sentence. The sentence begins with *al-ḥamd* — the praise — and it is worth recalling that this is the Qur'an's most fundamental utterance, the opening of its first chapter and the formula with which every sūrah but one begins. The two kings do not say "we are great." They say the phrase every Muslim says at the start of every prayer.

**Faḍḍalanā: Preference, and the Humility Built Into the Word**

The verb *faḍḍala* is Form II from *f-ḍ-l*, to give preference, to favor one above another, to make something exceed. The Qur'an uses this verb constantly and always in a specific theological register: preference is *given*, never earned as of right, and it is distributed by God's choice.

> *"And God has preferred some of you above others in provision."* (Qur'an 16:71)

> *"And verily We have preferred some of the prophets above others."* (Qur'an 17:55)

> *"And the men have a degree above them"* — in the context of marital responsibility (Qur'an 2:228)

Notice what David and Solomon do with the verb. They use it of themselves — *faḍḍalanā*, "He favored us" — and then they add a qualification that has been much discussed: *ʿalā kathīrin min ʿibādihi'l-muʾminīn*, "above *many* of His believing servants." They do not say "above all His servants." Al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, and al-Qurṭubī all note the restraint. Two men who had been given prophethood, kingdom, the language of birds, and command over the jinn — the highest concentration of favor in the Qur'an's history — described their preference as being above *many* believers, not above *all* of them.

The reason for the restraint is doctrinal and it is precise: among God's believing servants are the other prophets, and some of them hold a rank David and Solomon did not claim. Abraham is *khalīl*, the intimate friend of God (4:125). Moses is one to whom God spoke directly (*kalīm Allāh*, 4:164). Muhammad ﷺ is the seal. The Qur'an itself states the principle:

> *"And verily We have preferred some of the prophets above others, and We gave unto David the Psalms."* (Qur'an 17:55)

> *"That is the Bounty of God; He gives it unto whom He will. And God is Possessed of tremendous Bounty."* (Qur'an 62:4)

So the two kings, given knowledge, immediately demonstrated the knowledge they had been given: they knew where they stood. Their gratitude was accurate. And accuracy about one's own rank is, in the Qur'anic estimate, itself a form of knowledge — perhaps the rarest form, and the one most often missing in people who have everything else.

**Gratitude for Knowledge Before Gratitude for Kingdom**

The source commentary records a striking observation from al-Rāzī: David and Solomon's gratitude for their God-given knowledge surpasses their gratitude for their kingdom. The verse gives the order — knowledge mentioned, then praise offered — and the praise is directed at the favoring, which the verse has just defined as a favoring in knowledge.

This ordering inverts the ordinary human sequence. A king who has been given both a kingdom and understanding typically thanks God for the kingdom and takes the understanding for granted, because the kingdom is visible and the understanding is not. The verse presents the reverse and thereby teaches a lesson about what is actually valuable. A kingdom is a temporary arrangement of power that passes to heirs, enemies, or dust; knowledge is a permanent acquisition that changes the knower and, in the case of prophetic knowledge, changes the world. Solomon's kingdom was, by God's own arrangement, unrepeatable — he asked for *"a kingdom that belongs to no one after me"* (38:35) — and it ended with him. His knowledge did not end with him; it is in this sūrah, being read fourteen centuries later.

**The Believers Named in the Comparison**

One further detail deserves attention: the standard against which David and Solomon measure themselves is not the disbelievers or the ignorant but *God's believing servants*. They compare themselves to the best available comparison — the community of faith — and they find that they have been favored above many of that community. The humility is real precisely because the comparison group is exalted.

This is a discipline with direct practical application. The ordinary human habit is to measure oneself downward: against those who have less, those who know less, those who behave worse. The measurement always produces satisfaction and never produces growth. David and Solomon measured themselves against the believers — the people doing the same thing they were doing, worshiping the same Lord — and the measurement produced gratitude rather than pride. A student who compares himself to those who failed will never learn; a student who compares himself to those who mastered the subject will. A worshipper who compares himself to the negligent will feel accomplished; a worshipper who compares himself to the devout will feel the gap and close it.

The verse's two men had every worldly reason to compare downward. They chose not to, and the Qur'an records their sentence as the model response to being given anything at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:16

> **Solomon inherited from David and said, "O mankind, we have been taught the language of the birds, and we have been given of all things. Truly this is clear bounty!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Inheritance: What a Prophet Leaves Behind**

*Wa waritha Sulaymānu Dāwūd* — "And Solomon inherited from David." The verb is *waritha*, from *w-r-th*, to inherit, to succeed to property or position after the death of the one who held it. The verse is short and its implications have been extensively worked out, because the Muslim tradition is emphatic that prophets do not leave material estates. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record the Prophetic statement that settles the matter:

> *"We, the company of prophets, are not inherited from; whatever we leave is charity (ṣadaqah)."* (al-Bukhārī 3093; Muslim 1758, from Abū Bakr)

And:

> *"My property is not divided among my heirs; what I leave after the maintenance of my wives and the salary of my workers is charity."* (al-Bukhārī 2776)

Given this, the commentators are unanimous that the inheritance named in verse 16 is not wealth. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr identify it as an inheritance of *knowledge*, of *prophethood*, and of *kingdom* — and the verse itself confirms the first of these by what Solomon immediately says: "we have been *taught* the language of the birds." Al-Rāzī specifies that the knowledge of the birds' language was among the things Solomon inherited from David, citing:

> *"And verily We gave David bounty from Us: 'O mountains! Echo God's praises with him, and you birds!' And We made the iron soft for him."* (Qur'an 34:10)

> *"And We compelled the mountains and the birds to glorify along with David; and We ever do [such things]."* (Qur'an 21:79)

> *"And the birds gathered, each turning oft unto Him."* (Qur'an 38:19)

So David was given a relation to birds in which they glorified God with him; Solomon was given the further gift of understanding their speech. The inheritance is a *capacity*, passed from father to son, and the Qur'an uses the language of inheritance to make a point: what a believing parent actually transmits to a child is not property but disposition — a way of attending to the world.

There is also a selection in the inheritance. David had many sons, and the Qur'an does not say Solomon was the eldest. What it shows is that the son who received the knowledge was the son who had the capacity for it — the one whom 21:79 credits with *understanding of the case* when both father and son judged the dispute over the sheep. Inheritance in the religious sense is not automatic; it goes to the one who can carry it. The tradition's own language for this is *wirāthat al-ʿilm*, the inheritance of knowledge, and the Prophet ﷺ described it in a report recorded by Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī:

> *"The scholars are the heirs of the prophets, and the prophets left neither dinar nor dirham; they left only knowledge. So whoever takes it has taken a great share."* (Abū Dāwūd 3641; al-Tirmidhī 2682, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

Verse 16 is a Qur'anic illustration of that ḥadīth before the ḥadīth was spoken.

**"O Mankind": A King Addressing the Public**

*Wa qāla yā ayyuha'l-nāsu* — "and he said: O mankind!" The address is universal, not to his court or his army. Al-Qurṭubī notes that Solomon's announcement was public and deliberate: he did not treat his gift as a private advantage but declared it, so that people would know what God had given and would attribute it correctly.

There is wisdom in this that the verse implies. A capacity that no one knows about can be mistaken for sorcery — the very accusation verse 13 recorded against Moses' signs. By announcing publicly that he had been *taught*, Solomon placed his gifts on the record as divine instruction rather than acquired art. The passive verb is crucial: *ʿullimnā*, "we have been taught." He does not say "we know"; he says "we were taught." Knowledge in the Qur'an's idiom always has a teacher, and naming the teaching names the Teacher.

**Manṭiq al-Ṭayr: The Language of the Birds**

*ʿullimnā manṭiqa'l-ṭayr* — "we have been taught the language (*manṭiq*) of the birds." The word *manṭiq* is from *n-ṭ-q*, to speak, to utter — the same root that gives *nāṭiq*, speaking, and that Arabic uses for rational, articulate speech. Its application to birds is remarkable, and the commentators handled it in two ways.

The first takes it literally: Solomon understood the actual communications of birds. Al-Rāzī discusses whether animals have language in the ordinary sense and concludes that they possess a measure of intelligence and inspiration proper to their state of being — a position the Qur'an supports independently when it describes creatures as communities:

> *"And there is no creature that crawls upon the earth, nor a bird that flies on its two wings, but they are communities (umam) like unto you. We have neglected nothing in the Book; then unto their Lord they shall be gathered."* (Qur'an 6:38)

The phrase *umam amthālukum* — "communities like unto you" — is one of the Qur'an's most expansive statements about non-human life, and it is the ground on which the classical commentators could take the ant's speech in verse 18 seriously rather than as fable. If birds and ants are *communities*, they have social organization, communication, and a relation to God; and if God gave one prophet the capacity to understand that communication, the account is coherent.

The second reading is spiritual and it became enormously influential. The "language of the birds" entered Sufi vocabulary as a symbol for the knowledge of inner meanings — the speech behind the speech, the significance of things beyond their outward appearance. Some Sufis interpreted the phrase to mean not only esoteric knowledge generally but specifically the knowledge of the inner meaning of other religions. The most famous work in Persian mystical literature takes its title from this very phrase: Farīd al-Dīn ʿAṭṭār's *Manṭiq al-Ṭayr*, usually rendered "The Conference of the Birds" or "The Speech of the Birds," written in 1177 — the identical Arabic words as our verse. In ʿAṭṭār's poem the birds, led by the hoopoe, set out to find their king, and the journey is the soul's path to God. That a twelfth-century Persian poet could build an entire allegory on a phrase from this sūrah, and that the hoopoe of verses 20–28 becomes the guide of that allegory, is a measure of how deeply al-Naml entered the Muslim imagination.

**"We Have Been Given of All Things": The Phrase That Does Not Mean Everything**

*Wa ūtīnā min kulli shayʾ* — "and we have been given of all things." Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī both note that this does not mean literally everything, but "much" or "abundantly" — a common Arabic idiom in which *min kulli shayʾ* means "of every kind of thing [that is relevant]." The same phrase appears in verse 23, where the hoopoe reports of the Queen of Sheba that *she has been given of all things* — and there too it means everything a kingdom requires, not omniscience.

The idiom deserves notice because it reveals how the Qur'an handles hyperbole. It permits a speaker to use the language of totality in a context where the hearer understands the limit, and it does not treat this as falsehood. Solomon, describing his own endowment, uses the largest phrase available — and the Qur'an reports it approvingly, because the phrase is doing the work of *gratitude*, not of *boasting*. The very next clause makes the difference plain.

**"Truly This Is Clear Bounty": The Sentence That Governs the Whole Narrative**

*Inna hādhā la-huwa'l-faḍlu'l-mubīn* — "Truly this, this is the clear bounty." Three emphatic devices are stacked in six words: *inna* (truly), the *lām* of emphasis in *la-huwa*, and the separated pronoun *huwa* ("it is He/it is it") which in Arabic syntax produces exclusivity — "this, and nothing else, is the clear bounty." The construction is one of the strongest assertions of identification available in the language, and the Qur'an uses it elsewhere in exactly the same shape:

> *"Verily this — this is the true certainty (haqq al-yaqīn)."* (Qur'an 56:95)

> *"That Day the truth shall be whose is the Kingdom."* — cf. the same emphatic structure at 40:16, 56:95, 69:51

The identification Solomon makes is between everything he has just described — the birds' language, the abundance, the inherited kingdom — and a single noun: *faḍl*, bounty, unearned grace. The root *f-ḍ-l* is the same root as *faḍḍala* in verse 15, "He favored us." Father and son both arrive at the same word, and the word means: none of this is mine; all of it is excess given.

The Qur'an uses *faḍl* of God more than a hundred times, and it is one of the most important of its theological terms. *Faḍl* is grace that exceeds what is owed: God is not obliged to give anything, and everything given is *faḍl*. The verse's placement of the word at the end of Solomon's announcement is deliberate, and it sets up the entire Solomonic narrative that follows. When the Queen of Sheba sends gifts (v. 35), Solomon will refuse them with the words *"what God has given me is better than what He has given you"* (v. 36). When the throne arrives (v. 40), he will say *"this is of the bounty (faḍl) of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful."* The word established here in verse 16 becomes the key that unlocks every subsequent event: Solomon is a man who has correctly identified the source of what he has, and the rest of the story is the demonstration of what that correct identification makes possible.

A reader can test himself against the verse without any kingdom at all. Look at whatever you have — health, a language, a skill, a family, a mind that can follow an argument — and ask whether your sentence about it is "I earned this," "I was lucky," or "this is clear bounty." The three sentences produce three entirely different people. The first produces a person who cannot lose anything without being destroyed, because everything he has is his own achievement. The second produces a person who cannot be grateful, because luck owes nothing. The third produces Solomon: a man with an army of jinn who spends his life saying thank you, and who, when he loses everything at death, loses only what he had already handed back.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:17

> **And gathered for Solomon were his hosts of jinn and men and birds, and they were marshaled [in ordered ranks],**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Army Nobody Else Has Ever Had**

*Wa ḥushira li-Sulaymāna junūduhu mina'l-jinni wa'l-insi wa'l-ṭayri fa-hum yūzaʿūn* — "And gathered for Solomon were his hosts, of jinn and men and birds, and they were held in ranks." The verb *ḥushira* is the passive of Form I from *ḥ-sh-r*, to gather, to muster, to assemble a crowd for a purpose — the same root that gives *ḥashr*, the Gathering of the Day of Judgment, and *al-maḥshar*, the place of assembly. The Qur'an uses this root of the resurrection constantly:

> *"And [remember] the Day when We shall gather them all together"* (Qur'an 6:22, 10:28, 18:47)

> *"And the trumpet will be blown, and We shall gather the sinners on that Day, blue-eyed."* (Qur'an 20:102)

The passive form *ḥushira* — "there were gathered for Solomon" — is significant. Solomon did not assemble the army himself by effort; it was assembled *for him* (*li-Sulaymāna*), the *lām* indicating that the gathering was done on his behalf and for his benefit. The commentators read this as a mark of the miraculous character of the event: an army of three different orders of being, one of them invisible, one of them flying, gathered for one man. This was the kingdom Solomon had asked for, and the Qur'an elsewhere records the prayer:

> *"He said: 'My Lord! Forgive me and grant me a kingdom that belongs to no one after me. Truly Thou art the Bestower.'"* (Qur'an 38:35)
