The request was granted in the terms requested, and verse 17 is the description of the grant.

**Three Kinds of Creature, and What Each Implies**

The threefold composition — *jinn, ins, ṭayr* — has been carefully examined by the commentators, and each component carries a distinct theological implication.

**Men** (*al-ins*) are the ordinary element: soldiers, commanders, workers, artisans. Their presence establishes that this was a real polity with a real army, not a vision. Solomon ruled human beings in the ordinary way.

**Birds** (*al-ṭayr*) were, on the commentators' account, organized as units with functions. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī report that specific birds were assigned specific tasks — the hoopoe as scout, finding water and reporting on distant lands; the hoopoe's absence in verse 20 will show how the system worked. Al-Qurṭubī notes that birds served as scouts, couriers, and, on one report, as a screen against the sun. The inclusion of birds establishes the point made in verse 16: the *manṭiq al-ṭayr* was not an idle curiosity but an operational capacity, and it produced an intelligence corps no other king possessed.

**Jinn** (*al-jinn*) are the decisive element. Al-Rāzī and the source commentary both draw the conclusion: the inclusion of jinn indicates not only that the army was of a miraculous and wondrous nature, but that Solomon had the stature and power to control and employ beings ordinarily uncontrollable by human beings and most often mischievous. The Qur'an describes the jinn's character elsewhere and makes the point explicit:

> *"And [We subjected] unto Solomon the wind — its morning course a month, and its evening course a month — and We made a spring of molten copper flow for him. And among the jinn were those who worked before him by the leave of his Lord. And whosoever of them turned away from Our command, We let him taste the punishment of the Blaze."* (Qur'an 34:12)

> *"And the jinn: some made for him sanctuaries and statues, and basins like water-troughs, and cauldrons built in the ground. 'Work, O house of David, in gratitude.' And few of My servants are grateful."* (Qur'an 34:13)

> *"Say: 'It has been revealed unto me that a company of the jinn listened, and said: "Verily we have heard a wondrous Qur'an, guiding unto rectitude, and we have believed in it...""'* (Qur'an 72:1–2)

Verse 34:12 adds a detail of great significance: the jinn worked *bi-idhni rabbihi* — "by the leave of his Lord." Solomon's authority over them was not his own power but a delegated power, and the delegation came with a penalty clause for the jinn who disobeyed. This is why Solomon's control of the jinn is treated in the tradition as a unique, non-transferable grant — precisely because he had asked for a kingdom "that belongs to no one after me," and God gave it.

The theological purpose of the grant is stated in the ḥadīth cited at verse 6 of the introduction and worth repeating here in its function: the Prophet ﷺ, when an ʿifrīt of the jinn attacked him during his prayer, overpowered it and considered tying it to a pillar of the mosque for the people to see — and then released it, saying:

> *"...then I remembered the supplication of my brother Solomon: 'My Lord, forgive me and grant me a kingdom that belongs to no one after me' — so I let him go, humiliated."* (al-Bukhārī 461; Muslim 541)

The report establishes three things at once: that the jinn are real and can interact physically with human beings; that the Prophet ﷺ had the power to subdue one; and that he refrained out of respect for the boundary God had set at Solomon's request. Verse 17's army therefore sits inside a defined, unique, unrepeatable grant — which is why no later claimant to such authority in the Muslim world has ever been taken seriously by the scholars.

**Yūzaʿūn: The Word of Restraint**

The final word of the verse is *yūzaʿūn*, and it is one of the most precisely chosen verbs in the sūrah. It is the passive of Form I from *w-z-ʿ*, a root whose basic meaning is to restrain, to hold back, to prevent from advancing, to keep in order. Al-Ṭabarī records the interpretations: "corralled" or "kept in check," as in keeping the lines together and tightly grouped; others say it means they moved forward, or that they were defenders against attack; and al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both note the sense of being held back from disorder.

The dominant sense, and the one the translation follows, is *marshaled* — held in formation, restrained from breaking ranks. The commentators explain the necessity: an army containing men, birds, and jinn would, without restraint, be chaos. The men would move at walking pace, the birds at flight speed, the jinn at their own; the strong would crowd the weak; the flying would scatter. *Yūzaʿūn* describes the discipline imposed on all three: each held to its position, none advancing out of turn.

The theological implication is drawn by al-Rāzī and by later commentators. Restraint is what makes a multitude into an army. Power without order is not strength; it is noise. And the fact that the Qur'an uses a verb of *holding back* rather than a verb of *driving forward* to describe this army tells the reader what Solomon's greatness consisted in: not the ability to make things move, but the ability to make things *stay*.

The same verb returns in verse 19, in a completely different register — *awziʿnī an ashkura niʿmatak*, "inspire me to give thanks for Thy blessing" — and the connection between the two uses is one of the sūrah's most beautiful structural features. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *awzaʿnī* is literally the same word as *yūzaʿūn*, and that the difference in translation arises from context. In verse 17 it means "they are held in ranks"; in verse 19 it means "hold me to gratitude," "restrain me toward thankfulness," "make me such that gratitude is my settled formation." The verb of military discipline becomes the verb of spiritual discipline, and the sūrah thereby states its thesis: the greatest act of governance a man performs is over his own soul, and it uses the same instrument as the governance of an army — restraint, ordering, holding to position.

**The Word "Ḥashr" and the Eschatological Echo**

There is a further resonance in the verse that the classical commentators noted and that becomes clear only later in the sūrah. *Ḥushira* — "there were gathered" — is the verb of the Gathering, and al-Naml will use it again at verse 83:

> *"And on that Day We shall gather (naḥshuru) from every community a group of those who denied Our signs, and they shall be marshaled (yūzaʿūn)."* (Qur'an 27:83)

The same two words — *ḥashr* and *wazʿ* — appear in both verses. In verse 17 they describe an army of jinn, men, and birds gathered for a king and held in ranks. In verse 83 they describe the communities of deniers gathered before God and held in ranks. The sūrah is deliberately rhyming the two scenes: Solomon's muster is a small-scale image of the Muster, and the reader who has watched one army be gathered and marshaled has been prepared to imagine the other.

This is characteristic of al-Naml's method throughout. It does not merely tell stories; it constructs patterns and then reuses them at a higher scale. The ant's warning to her community (v. 18) will be echoed by the prophets' warnings to theirs. Solomon's inspection of his troops (v. 20) will be echoed by God's inspection of the gathered (v. 83–84). The throne brought in a glance (v. 40) will be echoed by the arrival of every soul with its deeds. The reader is being trained, verse by verse, in the vocabulary of the Last Day — using the vocabulary of a kingdom.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:18

> **till when they came to the valley of the ants, an ant said, "O ants! Enter your dwellings, lest Solomon and his hosts crush you, while they are unaware."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Named the Sūrah**

*Ḥattā idhā ataw ʿalā wādi'l-namli qālat namlatun yā ayyuha'l-namlu'dkhulū masākinakum lā yaḥṭimannakum Sulaymānu wa junūduhu wa hum lā yashʿurūn* — "Until, when they came upon the valley of the ants, an ant said: 'O ants! Enter your dwellings, lest Solomon and his hosts crush you, while they are unaware.'"

This is the verse from which the entire chapter takes its name, and the ninety-second verse of a ninety-three-verse sūrah is nowhere mentioned in it again. Al-Naml is called "The Ants" because of nineteen words. The disproportion is worth dwelling on before the exegesis begins, because it is the sūrah's own first lesson about scale.

**Wādi'l-Naml: A Valley Known by Its Smallest Inhabitants**

The place is described as *wād*, a valley — the same word used of the sacred valley of Ṭuwā where Moses stood (20:12), and of the valley where the people of Thamūd carved their houses (7:74, 15:82, 26:149). A valley in the Qur'an is a real geographical feature with a real name, and the commentators identify this one variously. Some report it was in the land of Syria, some in Yemen, some in the region of al-Ṭāʾif; al-Qurṭubī records the opinion that it was a known place whose name derived from the abundance of ants in it. The Qur'an does not settle the question, and al-Rāzī's methodological principle applies: where the text is silent, silence is the correct scholarly position.

More significant is the construction itself. The valley is named *after* the ants — *wād al-naml*, "the valley of the ants" — not the ants after the valley. In Arabic naming conventions, the *iḍāfah* (construct) attributes the thing to what is most characteristic of it. This valley's defining feature, in the Qur'an's account, is not its water, its trees, or its position on a trade route. It is that ants live there. And into that valley walks the greatest army the world has ever seen.

**"An Ant Said": The Grammatical Facts the Commentators Established**

*Qālat namlatun* — "an ant said." Three grammatical details in these two words have been carefully analyzed.

First, the verb is feminine: *qālat*, "she said." Arabic verbs agree in gender with their subject, and the Qur'an's use of the feminine here establishes that the ant is female. The commentators note that the ant's speech is reported in the feminine throughout — *qālat*, and in verse 19 Solomon smiles at *her words* (*qawlahā*). Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī discuss whether the gender marking is incidental or meaningful; al-Bayḍāwī observes that ant colonies are in fact organized around females — a point of natural observation that medieval commentators recorded without needing modern entomology to confirm it, and which the Qur'an's consistent feminine marking reflects.

Second, the noun is indefinite: *namlatun*, "an ant" — not "the ant." No name is given, no rank, no identification. Some commentators, as the source records, give a name for this ant, though others say it is inconceivable that one could possibly know such a detail. The Qur'an's own choice is the indefinite form, and the indefinite form is the point: an unnamed individual, of the smallest kind of creature, speaking to her community.

Third, the speech is direct quotation, introduced by the standard formula *qālat* followed by a vocative: *yā ayyuha'l-naml*, "O ants!" The vocative construction *yā ayyuhā* is the formal Arabic address used for a group — the identical construction used when God addresses human beings in the Qur'an: *yā ayyuha'l-nās* ("O mankind"), *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* ("O you who believe"). The ant addresses her community with the same grammatical dignity with which the Qur'an addresses its readers. There is no diminution, no parody, no marker that this is a lesser speech.

**"Enter Your Dwellings": The Ant's Words Examined**

*Udkhulū masākinakum* — "enter your dwellings." The word *maskan* (plural *masākin*) is from *s-k-n*, to be still, to settle, to come to rest — the same root as *sakīnah*, the tranquility God sends down upon believers (9:26, 48:4), and *maskan*, a dwelling. A *maskan* is a place where one comes to stillness. The ant's command is: go home, be still, get inside.

The plural noun and the plural verb are both addressed to the community, and the commentators note the ant's social consciousness: she does not say "I am going inside"; she says "enter your dwellings." She speaks as a member addressing members, in the register of public warning. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī observe the four elements of her speech, each of which is a component of competent leadership: she identifies the danger (an approaching army), she identifies the vulnerable party (her community), she prescribes the remedy (enter your dwellings), and she explains the mechanism of the danger (they will crush you without noticing).

**"Lest Solomon and His Hosts Crush You": The Realism of the Danger**

*Lā yaḥṭimannakum Sulaymānu wa junūduh* — "lest Solomon and his hosts crush you." The verb *yaḥṭimanna* is from *ḥ-ṭ-m*, to break, to shatter, to crush — with the *nūn* of emphasis (*nūn al-tawkīd*) attached, giving the sense of "lest they certainly crush you." *Al-Ḥuṭamah* is one of the names of Hellfire in the Qur'an (104:4–5), from the same root: the Crusher.

The ant's assessment of the danger is entirely accurate and entirely unromantic. A marching army does not target ants. It does not see them. An ant in the path of ten thousand soldiers and a column of jinn and a flight of birds will be destroyed, and destroyed incidentally, as a side effect of something else entirely. The ant understands this with perfect clarity, and her solution is not protest, negotiation, or appeal. It is evacuation.

**"While They Are Unaware": The Verse's Most Extraordinary Phrase**

*Wa hum lā yashʿurūn* — "while they are unaware." The verb *yashʿurūn* is from *sh-ʿ-r*, whose root sense is perception — related to *shaʿr*, hair, and to *shuʿūr*, feeling, awareness, sensation. *Sh-ʿ-r* in the Qur'an denotes the subtle, fine perception of something:

> *"And you were not beside the western side when We decreed unto Moses the command... but [We sent you] as a mercy from thy Lord, that thou mayest warn a people unto whom no warner came before thee, that haply they might take heed."* — cf. the usage at 28:44

> *"They did not perceive (mā shaʿarū) [what happened]"* — cf. 36:29, 12:11 where the root denotes unperceived events

The clause is the moral center of the verse, and the commentators have drawn out its implications at length. Al-Rāzī's observation, recorded in the source, is the key: the ant says *while they are unaware*, assuming that if a prophet like Solomon knew, he would not crush them. That is — the ant reasons about Solomon's character. She has never met him. She does not know his policies. But she knows, or assumes, that a being of his rank would not want to destroy her community, and that any destruction that occurs will be accidental rather than intended.

Three conclusions follow, and the Qur'an intends all three.

**First: no creature is beneath moral consideration.** The ant's presumption is that her community matters enough to be spared if the powerful can possibly spare it. The Qur'an affirms the presumption by reporting it without correction. Combined with 6:38 — *"there is no creature that crawls upon the earth, nor a bird that flies on its two wings, but they are communities like unto you"* — and with the Prophetic prohibition on killing ants, bees, hoopoes, and shrikes (Abū Dāwūd 5267; Ibn Mājah 3224), the verse establishes a moral universe considerably larger than the human one.

**Second: unintended harm is still harm, and the powerful are responsible for its possibility.** Solomon's army is not accused of anything. The ant does not say "they will attack us"; she says "they will crush us without noticing." The danger is precisely the danger of *unnoticed* harm — the harm that great power does incidentally, in the ordinary course of its business, to those too small to register. The ant's remedy — get out of the way — is a small creature's remedy, but the verse's warning is addressed to the great: your ordinary movement is a catastrophe to someone, and you will not feel it happen.

**Third: awareness is the moral faculty that verse 18 makes central.** The word *lā yashʿurūn* — they do not perceive — appears in the Qur'an dozens of times, almost always as a diagnosis of the disbelievers: they destroy and do not perceive; they are punished and do not perceive; they are given respite and do not perceive. Here it is applied, uniquely, as an *excuse*: Solomon's hosts will crush the ants *and they will not perceive it*, and the ant's own reasoning treats the non-perception as the mitigating factor. The verse thus states a principle the Muslim legal tradition developed explicitly: harm done without knowledge or intent is not the same as harm done deliberately — and yet the harm is real, and the remedy is real, and someone has to think about it. In this case, the someone is an ant.

**Why This Verse Is in a Sūrah About Power**

The structural placement is the interpretive key. Verse 17 describes the greatest army ever assembled. Verse 18 describes an ant. Verse 19 describes the commander of that army stopping, smiling, and praying that God make him grateful. The three verses form a single argument about power, and it is the sūrah's central argument.

Power that does not hear the ant is not greatness; it is only mass. What makes Solomon a prophet rather than a Pharaoh is not that he has an army — Pharaoh had one — but that he *perceived*. He heard a sentence spoken by an insect in a valley his column was crossing, and he did not treat the sentence as beneath his notice. He stopped. He smiled. He prayed. Verse 18 is the test that verse 19 reports him passing, and the whole Solomonic narrative that follows — the discipline of the hoopoe, the letter to a queen, the refusal of her gifts, the transport of her throne, the glass pavilion — is the elaboration of a character established in these two verses.

The modern application requires no translation. Every large institution — a government, a corporation, a hospital system, a university, a family — moves through valleys full of ants. Its ordinary operations crush things it does not perceive: a small business, a quiet employee, a neighbor's sleep, a child's confidence, an ecosystem, a community's language. The institution's leaders are not wicked; they are *lā yashʿurūn*, unaware. The Qur'an's answer is not that institutions should stop moving. It is that institutions should be governed by people who can hear an ant — and that the capacity to hear one is a gift of God to be prayed for, which is precisely what verse 19 will show Solomon doing.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:19

> **And he smiled, laughing at her words, and said, "My Lord! Inspire me to give thanks for Thy blessing wherewith Thou hast blessed me and my parents and to work righteousness pleasing to Thee, and cause me to enter, through Thy Mercy, among Thy righteous servants!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Longest Sentence Spoken by Solomon in the Qur'an**

*Fatabassama ḍāḥikan min qawlihā wa qāla Rabbi awziʿnī an ashkura niʿmataka'llatī anʿamta ʿalayya wa ʿalā wālidayya wa an aʿmala ṣāliḥan tarḍāhu wa adkhilnī bi-raḥmatika fī ʿibādika'l-ṣāliḥīn* — "So he smiled, laughing at her words, and said: 'My Lord! Inspire me to give thanks for Thy blessing wherewith Thou hast blessed me and my parents, and to work righteousness that pleases Thee, and cause me to enter, through Thy Mercy, among Thy righteous servants.'"

This is the response of the most powerful man in the Qur'an's history to the sight of an ant warning her neighbors. It contains no command to the army, no reflection on his own grandeur, no remark about the valley. It is a prayer about gratitude, righteousness, and company. The verse is the moral summit of the Solomonic narrative, and everything after it — the hoopoe, the queen, the throne — is the working-out of a character disclosed in this sentence.

**"He Smiled, Laughing": Two Verbs and Their Order**

*Fatabassama ḍāḥikan* — "so he smiled, laughing." Arabic distinguishes *tabassum* (smiling) from *ḍaḥik* (laughing), and the verse uses both: the finite verb is *smiled*, and the circumstantial qualifier (*ḥāl*) is *laughing* — "he smiled, in a state of laughter," that is, his smile broke into a laugh. The Qur'an's vocabulary of laughter is restrained: *ḍaḥik* appears in the Qur'an of Sarah's laugh at the angels' news (11:71), of the laughter of the people of the Garden (80:39, 83:34), of Solomon here, and of Lot's people's mockery. And *tabassum* appears here and of Solomon in no other place.

The commentators ask what he was laughing at, and their answers are careful. Al-Bayḍāwī records that he smiled because he marveled at the ant's anxiety and care, and felt grateful to God for the privilege of being able to understand the ant's concerns. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī add that he laughed at the disproportion — an insect giving a public safety briefing about the movements of a king — and at the ant's evident conviction that her warning mattered.

Two things are established by the smile. First, that Solomon found the ant's speech *good*. He was not irritated, not amused at her expense, not dismissive. The smile is the smile of a man who has recognized something admirable in a very small place. Second, that a prophet laughs. The Qur'an's portrait of its prophets is not a portrait of unbroken solemnity, and the tradition records the Prophet ﷺ smiling more often than any of his Companions. Al-Tirmidhī records from Jarīr ibn ʿAbdullāh:

> *"The Messenger of God ﷺ never veiled himself from me after I accepted Islam, and he never saw me but he smiled in my face."* (al-Bukhārī 3035; Muslim 2475)

And al-Tirmidhī records the description of his ordinary manner:

> *"The Messenger of God ﷺ was not coarse, nor did he shout in the markets, nor did he repay evil with evil; rather he pardoned and forgave."* (al-Tirmidhī 2016, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

A religion whose most powerful prophet is shown laughing at an ant's public announcement is a religion with a specific sense of humor about scale — and about the vanity of taking scale too seriously.

**Rabbi: The Address of a King Who Knows Who Is King**

The prayer begins with a single word: *Rabbī* — "my Lord." Not "O God of David," not "O Lord of the armies," not "O Mighty and Wise." *My* Lord. The possessive pronoun is the whole of the verse's theology in one syllable. Solomon, commanding jinn and birds and men, addresses the One who commands him, and he addresses Him as *his*. The Qur'an's prophets consistently begin their supplications this way — *rabbanā* (our Lord), *rabbi* (my Lord) — and the form establishes the relation before the request: the one praying is a servant, and the one prayed to is the Owner.

**Awziʿnī: The Verb of Holding in Formation**

*Rabbi awziʿnī an ashkura niʿmatak* — "My Lord, inspire me to give thanks for Thy blessing." The verb *awziʿnī* is Form IV from *w-z-ʿ*, and as al-Ṭabarī notes, it is literally the same word rendered *marshaled* in verse 17 — *yūzaʿūn*. In verse 17 the passive means "they were held in ranks, restrained from breaking formation." Here the imperative means, in effect, "hold me in place at gratitude," "gather me to it," "restrain me toward it," "make thanksgiving my settled formation."

Al-Ṭabarī records the simple gloss "make me" (*jaʿalnī*), while others explain the fuller sense: to inspire, to incite, to withhold a person from everything except the thing named — so that gratitude becomes the state he cannot leave. The commentators note that the difference in English translation between verses 17 and 19 arises entirely from context, and that recognizing the identity of the two words unlocks the verse.

This is one of the most beautiful structural devices in the sūrah. The army of jinn and men and birds is *yūzaʿūn* — held in formation by discipline imposed from above. The prophet's own soul is *awziʿnī* — asking to be held in the formation of gratitude by God. The word that described the ordering of an army is reused, in the very next verse, to describe the ordering of a heart. And the implication is precise: gratitude is not a feeling that arises spontaneously in response to a blessing. It is a *formation* — a discipline, a held position, something one is kept in, and something one must ask to be kept in.

The psychology underneath is universally recognized. A person who receives a great gift and does not practice gratitude will, within a short time, experience the gift as normal — this is the ordinary human adaptation by which every new comfort becomes invisible within months. The blessing does not change; the perception does. Solomon, who has just been given the greatest kingdom in history, does not trust his own perception to remain accurate. He asks God to hold him in the state. That request is the single most important sentence in the verse for any reader who has ever stopped noticing what he has.

**"The Blessing Wherewith Thou Hast Blessed Me and My Parents"**

*Al-latī anʿamta ʿalayya wa ʿalā wālidayya* — "which Thou hast blessed me with, and my parents." Al-Rāzī observes that Solomon mentioned his parents because he considered the invocation of blessings upon them to be tantamount to blessings upon himself. The reasoning is not sentimental; it is genealogical and honest. Solomon's gifts were inherited — verse 16 said so explicitly, *wa waritha Sulaymānu Dāwūd* — and the capacity to receive them was formed in a household before he formed it himself. To thank God for the gift without thanking Him for the parents who carried it is to thank Him for the fruit and not the root.

The Qur'an makes this connection a command elsewhere:

> *"And We have enjoined upon man kindness to his parents. His mother bore him in weakness upon weakness, and his weaning is in two years: 'Give thanks unto Me and unto thy parents; unto Me is the journey's end.'"* (Qur'an 31:14)

> *"And lower unto them the wing of humility out of mercy, and say: 'My Lord! Have mercy upon them both, as they raised me up when I was small.'"* (Qur'an 17:24)

Notice that 31:14 joins gratitude to God and gratitude to parents in a single imperative — *anishkur lī wa li-wālidayk* — exactly as Solomon's prayer does. And 17:24 supplies the duʿāʾ that a Muslim says for his parents, which is structurally identical to the second half of verse 19: an acknowledgment of what was done for one when one was small. Solomon's prayer is therefore not only a king's prayer; it is a son's prayer, and every Muslim who recites 17:24 after a parent's death is praying in the same shape.

**"And to Work Righteousness That Pleases Thee"**

*Wa an aʿmala ṣāliḥan tarḍāhu* — "and that I may work righteousness which Thou art pleased with." The clause adds a second request after gratitude, and it is not a redundant one. Gratitude is a state; *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* is an action. The Arabic *ṣāliḥ* is from *ṣ-l-ḥ*, soundness, wholeness, fitness, that which is in good repair and good order — the same root as *iṣlāḥ*, setting aright, which verse 48 will deny to Thamūd's nine conspirators. A *ṣāliḥ* deed is a deed that is sound, whole, and right in its construction.

The qualifying clause *tarḍāhu* — "that Thou art pleased with it" — is the critical element. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī note that Solomon does not ask to do righteous deeds; he asks to do righteous deeds *that God is pleased with*. The distinction separates the deed's outward form from its acceptance, and acceptance depends on two conditions the tradition identifies from the Qur'an itself: sincerity (*ikhlāṣ*) — that the deed be for God alone — and correctness (*muwāfaqah*) — that it conform to what God has legislated. The Qur'an states the pair:

> *"That He might test you, [to see] which of you is best in deed."* (Qur'an 67:2)

and al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ's famous gloss, preserved throughout the commentary tradition: "best in deed" means the most sincere and the most correct — for a sincere deed that is incorrect is not accepted, and a correct deed that is insincere is not accepted.

Solomon's phrasing thus contains the entire science of action in four words. He does not ask to do much; he asks to do what pleases. And the plea implies his own uncertainty: a prophet, at the height of his power, does not assume that his works are acceptable. He asks that they be made so. This is the same posture verse 11 described as the mark of sincerity — that one fears one's repentance is not complete — and it stands in direct contrast to the exultation (*ʿuluww*) of verse 14 that destroyed Pharaoh.

Al-Rāzī adds a further distinction: to work righteousness pertains to the life of this world, while the next clause — being included among God's righteous servants — pertains to being in their company in the Hereafter. The prayer therefore spans both worlds in its final two requests.

**"Cause Me to Enter, Through Thy Mercy, Among Thy Righteous Servants"**

*Wa adkhilnī bi-raḥmatika fī ʿibādika'l-ṣāliḥīn* — "and admit me, by Thy mercy, among Thy righteous servants." Three elements are worth separating.

The verb *adkhilnī* — "cause me to enter" — is the same verb used of entering the Garden:

> *"And it shall be said unto those who revered [their Lord]: 'What has your Lord sent down?' They shall say: 'Good.' For those who do good in this world there is good, and truly the abode of the Hereafter is better. And how excellent is the abode of the reverent — Gardens of Eden which they shall enter."* — cf. 16:30–31

> *"O soul at peace! Return unto thy Lord, well-pleased, well-pleasing. Enter thou among My servants, and enter thou My Garden."* (Qur'an 89:27–30)

The parallel with 89:29–30 is exact: *udkhulī fī ʿibādī wa'dkhulī jannatī* — "enter thou among My servants, and enter thou My Garden." Solomon's prayer asks for the first half of that sentence, and the Qur'an's own eschatology supplies the second.

The preposition *bi-raḥmatika* — "by Thy mercy" — is the most important word in the clause, and it makes a theological statement that the tradition considered foundational. Solomon does not ask to be admitted among the righteous *because of* his righteousness, his kingdom, his gratitude, or his deeds. He asks to be admitted *by God's mercy*. The ḥadīth that establishes the principle for the whole ummah is in al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"No one's deeds will admit him into the Garden." They said: "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: "Not even me, unless God enfolds me in mercy and grace from Him."* (al-Bukhārī 5673; Muslim 2816)

This is the doctrine of salvation in Islam stated in its purest form: entry into the Garden is by mercy, not by merit; and the deeds are the *cause* by which mercy is attracted, not the *price* by which the Garden is bought. Al-Ghazālī and Ibn Taymiyyah both expound the distinction at length. Solomon — a prophet, a king, a man who has just been shown thanking God correctly — prays for admission by mercy. The prayer is thus a rebuke to every form of religious self-sufficiency, and it comes from the least likely mouth in the Qur'an to need one.

The final phrase *fī ʿibādika'l-ṣāliḥīn* — "among Thy righteous servants" — asks for *company*. Not a rank, not a throne in Paradise, not a distinguishing station. Company. The commentators note that Solomon, who had been given a kingdom belonging to no one after him, asked at the end of his prayer for the ordinary thing: to be counted with the good. Al-Qurṭubī observes that this is the request of a man who understands what will actually matter. And the Qur'an records the same request from Joseph, at the summit of his own career as a ruler of Egypt:

> *"My Lord! Thou hast given me of sovereignty and taught me the interpretation of reports. Originator of the heavens and the earth, Thou art my Protector in this world and the Hereafter. Cause me to die a submitter, and join me with the righteous."* (Qur'an 12:101)

*Wā'alḥiqnī bi'l-ṣāliḥīn* — "join me with the righteous" — is the same petition in the same grammar from the other great prophet-king of the Qur'an. Two men with kingdoms, and both end their recorded prayers by asking not for more but for company. That coincidence is the sūrah's own commentary on power.

**The Duʿāʾ That Became the Ummah's Daily Practice**

Verse 19 did not remain inside the story. The Prophet ﷺ took its structure and taught it as a practice for his Companions, in the ḥadīth of Muʿādh ibn Jabal recorded by Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī:

> *"The Messenger of God ﷺ took my hand and said: 'O Muʿādh, by God, I love you. I advise you, O Muʿādh, never to omit saying at the end of every prayer: Allāhumma aʿinnī ʿalā dhikrika wa shukrika wa ḥusni ʿibādatika — O God, help me to remember You, to thank You, and to worship You well.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 1522; al-Nasāʾī 1303; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The correspondence is close and instructive. Solomon asks to be *held in* gratitude (*awziʿnī an ashkura*); Muʿādh is taught to ask to be *helped in* gratitude (*aʿinnī ʿalā... shukrik*). Solomon asks to work righteousness that pleases God (*an aʿmala ṣāliḥan tarḍāhu*); Muʿādh is taught to ask for good worship (*ḥusni ʿibādatik*). Both prayers concede the same premise: the servant cannot produce gratitude or good action from his own resources, and must ask for them as gifts.

Every Muslim who has recited that duʿāʾ after the five daily prayers — and it is standard practice across the schools — has been repeating the substance of verse 19. The sūrah's portrait of a king praying about an ant has become the ordinary believer's daily sentence.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:20

> **And he surveyed the birds and said, "How is it that I do not see the hoopoe? Or is he among those who are absent?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Inspection**

*Wa tafaqqada'l-ṭayra fa-qāla mā liya lā arā'l-hudhud a am kāna mina'l-ghāʾibīn* — "And he inspected the birds, and said: 'What is [the matter] with me, that I do not see the hoopoe? Or is he among those absent?'"

The verse moves from prayer to administration in a single word, and the word is *tafaqqada*. It is Form V from *f-q-d*, to miss, to lose, to notice the absence of something one expected to find. Form V adds the sense of deliberate, attentive seeking: *tafaqqud* is the act of going through a group specifically to find who is missing. The Qur'an uses the root rarely, and the classical lexicons gloss *tafaqqada'l-amr* as "he examined the affair carefully, seeking what was absent from it."

So the verse says: Solomon went through his birds, checking them, and noticed a gap. Al-Qurṭubī draws the leadership lesson explicitly and records it as one of the qualities of a great commander: he was aware of the condition of those he led, down to that of a single bird. The observation is not sentimental. It is a statement about what governance actually consists of — the noticing of absences.

**Why the Hoopoe Was Missed**

The commentators give two reasons for Solomon's noticing, and both are plausible readings of the narrative.

Al-Ṭabarī reports that the hoopoe was known for locating water — that it could see water beneath the ground, a property the classical naturalists attributed to the bird — and that Solomon had asked about water and found the hoopoe absent. On this reading the absence was operationally significant: an army crossing arid country needs its water-scout.

Al-Rāzī reports another account: the hoopoe's function was to shield Solomon from the sun with its wings, and Solomon noticed the absence because he felt the sun. This reading is more personal — the missing creature was missed by its commander's body, not only by his logistics.

Either way, the verse establishes a system in which each creature had a defined role, and the roles were known to the one in command. An army containing men, jinn, and birds cannot function unless someone knows what each unit is for. Solomon knew.

**Mā Liya Lā Arā: "What Is the Matter With Me, That I Do Not See"**

The phrasing of Solomon's question has been much admired by the commentators for its courtesy. He does not say "Where is the hoopoe?" or "Why is the hoopoe missing?" He says: *mā liya lā arā'l-hudhud* — "What is [the matter] with me, that I do not see the hoopoe?" Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī analyze the construction: Solomon attributes the deficiency to himself first, as though the absence might be a failure of his own sight or attention rather than of the bird's attendance.

This is a deliberate feature of the Qur'an's portrayal of prophetic speech, and it has a name in the tradition: *adab*, refined courtesy. A leader who says "what is wrong with me that I do not see X" leaves room for X to be innocent. A leader who says "why is X not here" has already begun the accusation. Solomon's phrasing is the first, and the second half of the verse completes it: *a am kāna mina'l-ghāʾibīn* — "or is he among the absent?" — supplying the innocent explanation himself. "Either my sight has failed me, or he is genuinely not here."

Al-Qurṭubī notes the legal implication drawn from this verse by later jurists: a ruler must verify before punishing, and the verification must be conducted in a manner that presumes the possibility of a legitimate excuse. Solomon will say in the very next verse that he will punish severely or slaughter the hoopoe — and he will add, in the same breath, *"unless he brings me a clear warrant."* The threat and the provision for excuse are in one sentence, and the sentence's order matters: the excuse comes as the condition of the punishment, not as an afterthought.

**Al-Hudhud: The Bird**

The *hudhud* — the hoopoe (*Upupa epops*) — is a medium-sized bird with a long thin curved bill, a distinctive erectile crest of feathers on its head, and black-and-white barred wings. It is widespread across the Middle East, North Africa, and Europe, and it is a bird of open country that probes the ground for insects. In the Arabic tradition the hoopoe was credited with keen sight, with the ability to detect water underground, and — because of these properties — with a place in folklore and in divination.

The Qur'an mentions the hoopoe only here, in verses 20 and 28 of this sūrah (and the bird is referenced again in the narrative through verse 44). Its selection as the agent of the greatest discovery in the story is deliberate. It is not an eagle, not a falcon, not a bird of prey or of rank. It is a modest, crested, ground-probing bird of the fields. And the news it brings — the existence of a wealthy queen worshiping the sun, two thousand miles away — changes the religious history of southern Arabia.

The Prophet ﷺ's prohibition on killing the hoopoe (Abū Dāwūd 5267; Ibn Mājah 3224; Aḥmad, from Ibn ʿAbbās: *"The Prophet ﷺ forbade the killing of four kinds of animal: the ant, the bee, the hoopoe, and the shrike"*) has been understood by the commentators as connected to this narrative. Ibn ʿAbbās himself is the narrator of the ḥadīth, and the pairing of the ant and the hoopoe in the prohibition matches their pairing in this sūrah. The two creatures of al-Naml are the two creatures the Prophet ﷺ named first in his list of those not to be killed.

**The Self-Questioning of a Prophet: "How Is It That I Do Not See"**

There is a further dimension in Solomon's phrasing that the Sufi commentators developed, and which has independent weight. Al-Qushayrī and Ibn ʿAjībah read the passage as an allegory of the relation between the spiritual heart and the soul's faculties: Solomon is the spiritual heart that governs, the hoopoe is a faculty of the soul — the one that travels far and returns with knowledge of what is not visible from home — and the heart notices when that faculty has gone missing.

The allegory describes a real phenomenon in the interior life. Every person has faculties that bring him knowledge from a distance: the capacity to imagine a life he has not lived, to consider the perspective of someone unlike himself, to foresee a consequence not yet arrived, to sense that something in his arrangements is wrong. These faculties travel. They leave, they are absent for long periods, and when they are absent the person does not notice their absence — he only notices that his decisions have become locally determined, based on what is in front of him.

Solomon's question — *what is the matter with me that I do not see the hoopoe?* — is the question a person asks when he realizes that a capacity he used to have has gone missing. The Qur'an presents it as the first move of a good ruler: not the accusation of the subordinate, but the examination of oneself. Before he threatens the bird, Solomon suspects his own eyesight. The order is the whole of the lesson.

**The Absent One Is Not the Forgotten One**

The verse ends with the possibility that the hoopoe is *mina'l-ghāʾibīn* — "among those who are absent." The word *ghāʾib* is from *gh-y-b*, absence, the same root as *ghayb*, the Unseen, which verse 3 of this sūrah and verse 65 will discuss at length. The hoopoe is *ghāʾib* — absent, unseen, off somewhere the commander cannot see.

The sūrah's structure turns on this absence. The hoopoe is missing, and because it is missing, Solomon asks about it; because he asks about it, the hoopoe returns with news; because it returns with news, a queen's kingdom is confronted; because it is confronted, a nation stops worshiping the sun. The entire second half of al-Naml proceeds from the fact that a small bird was not where it was expected to be, and that a king noticed.

The reader's application is straightforward and uncomfortable. Most of the harm done by institutions is done by absences nobody inspects: the student who stopped attending, the employee who went quiet, the parent who has not called, the clause nobody read, the risk nobody owned. Solomon's practice — *tafaqqud*, the deliberate search for what is missing — is the discipline that catches these. It costs time. It requires going through the ranks one by one. And in this sūrah it produces, in the end, the conversion of a queen.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:21

> **I will surely punish him with a severe punishment, or I shall slaughter him, unless he brings me a clear warrant."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Threat With a Built-In Exit**

*La-uʿadhdhibannahu ʿadhāban shadīdan aw la-adhbāḥannahu aw layaʾtiyannī bi-sulṭānin mubīn* — "I shall surely punish him with a severe punishment, or I shall surely slaughter him, unless he brings me a clear warrant."

The verse is constructed with an unusual precision that the commentators dissected. It contains two emphatic oaths of punishment, joined by *aw* ("or"), and then a third clause introduced by *aw* again, this time in its exceptive sense: "unless he brings me a clear warrant." The first two verbs carry the doubled *lām* and *nūn* of emphasis — *la-uʿadhdhibannahu*, *la-adhbāḥannahu* — "I shall *certainly* punish him," "I shall *certainly* slaughter him." Arabic stacks these particles when a speaker intends to remove all doubt about his resolve.

And then the verse removes the ground for exercising it. The threat is absolute and the exception is explicit, in the same sentence. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī both note that this is the grammar of just authority: the penalty is stated at full strength so that its seriousness is not in question, and the excuse is stated in the same breath so that the penalty is never applied without verification. A ruler who threatens vaguely punishes arbitrarily. A ruler who threatens precisely, and names the condition of release, punishes only on evidence.

**Sulṭān Mubīn: The Warrant That Makes Things Clear**

The word *sulṭān* is from *s-l-ṭ*, power, dominion, authority — the same root as *sulṭah*, authority, and *musalliṭ*, one given power over another. In the Qur'an, *sulṭān* most often means a decisive proof or authorization, and al-Ṭabarī glosses it here as clear evidence or a legitimate excuse for the hoopoe's absence.

The pairing *sulṭān mubīn* — a clear warrant, a manifest authority — is a Qur'anic formula used repeatedly of prophetic evidence itself:

> *"And We sent Moses with Our signs and a clear warrant (sulṭānin mubīn)."* (Qur'an 11:96; 23:45; 40:23)

> *"Say: 'Have you considered what you call upon apart from God? Show me what they have created in the earth, or do they have a share in the heavens? Bring me a Book before this, or a remnant of knowledge, if you are truthful.'"* — cf. 46:4

> *"Or have they taken gods apart from Him? Say: 'Bring your proof (burhānakum).'"* (Qur'an 21:24)

That Solomon uses for a bird's excuse the same phrase the Qur'an uses for a prophet's credentials is not a coincidence of vocabulary. It states the standard: an absence from duty requires justification of the same quality as a claim requires evidence — something clear, something that carries its own authority, something that can be examined. Not a story. Not an appeal to sympathy. A *warrant*.

**Punishment, Slaughter, and the Question of Proportion**

The two penalties named are graded, and the commentators discussed the gradation. *ʿAdhāban shadīdan* — a severe punishment — is the lesser; *dhabḥ*, slaughter, is the greater. Al-Ṭabarī records that some commentators understood the punishment to mean plucking the bird's feathers and exposing it to the sun, and others understood it as banishment from the ranks; the slaughter is self-explanatory. The gradation follows the ordinary legal principle that the penalty should match the offense, and the offense here has not yet been determined.

The deeper question the commentators raise is what kind of crime the hoopoe's absence could possibly be. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī answer that Solomon's authority over the birds was not merely ownership but a commissioned governance: the birds were part of a prophetic administration, and absence from that administration was absence from a duty assigned by God. On this reading the hoopoe's unexplained absence was not a pet's wandering off; it was a soldier's unauthorized absence from a prophet's service, and the seriousness of the threat reflects the seriousness of the office.

This explains why the threat is so severe and why the excuse is so readily accepted. The severity establishes the value of the duty; the readiness of the excuse establishes the value of the truth. When the hoopoe returns with a genuine warrant — a true report about a nation in idolatry — Solomon accepts it immediately (v. 27: *"We shall see if you have spoken the truth"*), and the threat is never mentioned again.

**"None Love Excuses More Than God"**

Al-Qurṭubī cites at this verse a ḥadīth that he takes to be the theological ground of the whole exchange:

> *"None loves excuses (maʿādhīr) more than God. That is why He revealed the Books and sent the messengers."* (al-Bukhārī 7416; Muslim 1499, from Ibn Masʿūd, in a longer narration on the believer's jealousy and God's)

The report is remarkable in its reasoning. It states that the *purpose* of revelation and prophethood is to make excuses unnecessary — to remove from human beings the plea of ignorance, so that they cannot say on the Day of Judgment that no one told them. And it grounds that purpose in a divine attribute: God *loves* excuses, in the sense that He loves to have them available to His servants, loves to accept them, and loves the state in which a servant has a legitimate warrant to present.

> *"Lest the people should have an argument against God after the messengers."* (Qur'an 4:165)

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

Solomon's sentence — *unless he brings me a clear warrant* — is a small-scale enactment of the same principle. The commander of an army of jinn, men, and birds does not accept an unexplained absence from a bird, and he does not punish a bird who has an explanation. He demands the warrant, and he leaves the door open for it. That is the shape of divine governance reproduced in the governance of a prophet-king, and it is why the verse has been cited in the Islamic legal tradition as a text on the necessity of hearing the accused before judgment.

**The Discipline the Verse Teaches About Anger**

There is a psychological reading of the verse that the commentators did not develop at length but that the text supports. Solomon is, on the surface of the verse, angry. The emphatic particles — *I shall surely, I shall surely* — are the language of a man whose temper has been engaged. And yet the anger produces a sentence with an exit clause in it.

This is a distinction of considerable practical value. Anger that produces a threat without an exit is the anger that destroys: it announces a consequence and then cannot withdraw it without losing face, so the consequence is inflicted on someone who had a good reason. Anger that produces a threat *with* an exit is the anger of a person who is upset but still fair — who wants the offense to stop and wants the truth to be heard, and who has structured his own anger so that it cannot operate without information.

The Prophet ﷺ legislated in this direction explicitly. Al-Bukhārī records the famous exchange:

> *"A man said to the Prophet ﷺ: 'Advise me.' He said: 'Do not become angry.' The man repeated his request several times, and each time he said: 'Do not become angry.'"* (al-Bukhārī 6116)

And al-Tirmidhī records the definition of strength that governs this verse:

> *"The strong man is not the one who overcomes people by his strength; the strong man is the one who controls himself at the time of anger."* (al-Bukhārī 6114; Muslim 2609)

Solomon in verse 21 is the Qur'an's illustration of the second ḥadīth. He is a man of absolute power, genuinely provoked, and his provocation issues in a demand for evidence rather than in an act of force. The verse does not rebuke him for the threat. It records it, along with the exception, as the model of angry authority that remains just.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:22

> **But he tarried not long, and said, "I have comprehended that which thou hast not comprehended, and I bring you a sure report from Sheba."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return, and the Speed of It**

*Fa-makatha ghayra baʿīdin fa-qāla aḥaṭtu bimā lam tuḥiṭ bihī wa jiʾtuka min sabaʾin bi-khabarin yaqīn* — "But he remained not long, and said: 'I have comprehended what thou hast not comprehended, and I have come to thee from Sheba with a certain report.'"

The phrase *fa-makatha ghayra baʿīd* — "he tarried not long" — is literally "he remained not far," that is, not for a long time. *Makatha* is from *m-k-th*, to stay, to remain, to linger, to be slow. The Qur'an uses the word of God's own deliberate pace with human beings:

> *"And He is the One who delays them (yuʾakhkhiruhum) for a term appointed"* — cf. 14:10

and of Moses' waiting at the mountain: *"And We appointed for Moses thirty nights, and completed them with ten, so the term of his Lord was completed at forty nights"* (7:142), where the sense of appointed duration governs. Here the phrase means the hoopoe's absence was brief in the telling — the narrative does not dwell on it, and the bird returns quickly.

The brevity has a function. Verse 21 ended with a threat. Verse 22 begins with the return. The gap between them is described as short, and the effect is to remove the threat's suspense without resolving it: the hoopoe is back, and the warrant is still to be examined. The Qur'an does not tell us Solomon forgave him; it tells us Solomon listened to him, which is the same thing in practice.

**Aḥaṭtu: The Verb of Encompassing Knowledge**

*I have comprehended* renders *aḥaṭtu*, from the root *ḥ-w-ṭ*, to surround, to enclose, to encompass — the same root as *ḥāʾiṭ*, a wall, and *muḥīṭ*, surrounding. Form IV, *aḥāṭa*, means to encompass something with knowledge, to comprehend it fully, to take it in on all sides.

The Qur'an reserves this verb for comprehensive knowledge and uses it most often of God:

> *"God encompasses (yuḥīṭu) all things in knowledge."* (Qur'an 65:12; 4:126; 8:47)

> *"And they encompass it not in knowledge (wa lā yuḥīṭūna bihī ʿilman)"* — of God's knowledge being beyond theirs (Qur'an 20:110)

> *"And what has been given you of knowledge is but little."* (Qur'an 17:85)

And at verse 84 of this very sūrah, God will ask the gathered disbelievers: *"Did you deny My signs, though you encompassed them not in knowledge (wa lam tuḥīṭū bihā ʿilman)?"* — using the identical verb to indict them for having denied what they had not comprehended.

The hoopoe's use of *aḥaṭtu* is therefore audacious and precise. It says: I have encompassed something that you have not encompassed. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the hoopoe had made a categorical claim in verse 22 about what it knew and what Solomon did not know — and that this is why Solomon responds in verse 27 with *"We shall see if you have spoken the truth or if you are among the liars."* A subordinate telling a prophet-king that the prophet-king lacks knowledge he possesses is an extraordinary speech act, and the narrative treats it as such: it must be verified.

**The Courtesy of the Claim**

Commentators have observed that the hoopoe's phrasing, though bold, is not insulting. It does not say "you are ignorant" or "I am wiser than you." It says "I have comprehended what you have not comprehended" — a statement about a specific item of knowledge, not about comparative worth. And the claim is immediately followed by its evidence: *wa jiʾtuka min sabaʾin bi-khabarin yaqīn* — "and I have come to you from Sheba with a certain report."

The structure is that of a well-formed report in any disciplined organization: the statement of what is being claimed, followed immediately by its source and its character. The hoopoe names the source (*from Sheba*) and names the epistemic quality (*a certain report*). This is why Solomon's response is not anger but verification — the report is presented in a form that can be verified, and a report that can be verified deserves the chance to be.

There is a lesson here about how to speak truth to power that requires no elaboration. The hoopoe does not flatter, does not preface with apologies, does not bury the claim. It states what it knows, states where it knows it from, and states the confidence level. And the king, who had threatened to slaughter it an hour earlier, listens.

**Khabar Yaqīn: A Report of Certainty**

*Bi-khabarin yaqīn* — "with a report of certainty" or "a sure report." Two nouns in construct, and both are technical.

*Khabar* is a report, a piece of news — in Arabic grammar and logic, a *khabar* is a statement that can be true or false, as opposed to *inshāʾ*, a statement that creates rather than reports (a command, a question, a wish). The Qur'an uses *khabar* of its own reports of the past:

> *"That is of the tidings (anbāʾ) of the Unseen which We reveal unto thee; thou didst not know it, neither thou nor thy people, before this."* (Qur'an 11:49)

> *"And We relate unto thee of the tidings (nabaʾ) of Moses and Pharaoh in truth, for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 28:3)

*Yaqīn* is the certainty discussed at verse 3 — the conviction that reorganizes action. The construct *khabar yaqīn* means a report whose certainty is intrinsic: not a rumor, not an inference, not a secondhand account. Al-Rāzī notes that the hoopoe distinguishes its report from the ordinary travel-tales that a bird might bring; it claims eyewitness certainty about a matter of religion.

The pairing matters for the sūrah's argument. Verse 3 defined the believers as those *certain of the Hereafter* — *hum bi'l-ākhirati hum yūqinūn*. Verse 4 defined the disbelievers as those whose deeds are adorned and who wander confused. Now a bird arrives with a *report of certainty* about a nation that has no certainty at all — a nation whose certainty is misplaced, directed at the sun. The word *yaqīn* is doing structural work across the sūrah, marking who has it, who lacks it, and what it is about.

**Sabaʾ: The Place Named**

*Sabaʾ* is the ancient South Arabian kingdom whose center was Mārib, in what is now Yemen, and whose civilization built the great Mārib dam, the incense trade routes, and the temples recorded in inscriptions from the eighth century BCE onward. The Qur'an mentions Sabaʾ by name in the sūrah of the same name:

> *"Verily there was for Sabaʾ a sign in their dwelling place: two gardens on the right hand and the left. 'Eat of the provision of your Lord and give thanks unto Him. A good land, and a forgiving Lord.' But they turned away, so We sent upon them the flood of the dam, and We replaced their two gardens with two gardens of bitter fruit and tamarisk and a few lote-trees. That We repaid them for their ingratitude. And do We repay any save the ungrateful?"* (Qur'an 34:15–17)

The connection between 34:15–17 and 27:22–44 is close and deliberate. Sūrah Sabaʾ describes the same civilization's wealth — two gardens, provision, a good land — and its end: they turned away, they were ungrateful, and the dam broke. Al-Naml describes the same civilization at the moment before: a queen, a mighty throne, everything given. The two sūrahs are the before and after of one history, and the hinge between them is the gratitude that Solomon's prayer at verse 19 asked for and that Sabaʾ, in the end, did not give.

The Islamic tradition names the queen Bilqīs (or Bilqays) daughter of Sharāḥīl, and the commentators discuss the name's origin at length — whether it is Arabic, Sabean, or Yemenite. The Qur'an itself does not name her, which the commentators note as characteristic: the Qur'an names few women (Mary, and by allusion the wives of prophets), and where a woman is central to a narrative without being named, the narrative is about her function rather than her person. This one is about a ruler making a decision.

**What the Hoopoe Brings: News as an Instrument of God**

The verse's theological significance lies in the disproportion it sets up, which the following twenty verses will exploit. A bird — absent without leave, threatened with slaughter, of no rank among Solomon's hosts — returns carrying the information that will lead an entire kingdom out of sun-worship. The Qur'an is explicit elsewhere that God uses small agents for large purposes and that He is not constrained by the apparent fitness of the instrument:

> *"And thy Lord creates whatsoever He wills and chooses; the choice is not theirs."* (Qur'an 28:68)

> *"And We inspired the mother of Moses: 'Suckle him; then, when thou fearest for him, cast him into the river and fear not, nor grieve; verily We shall return him unto thee and make him among the messengers.'"* (Qur'an 28:7)

A basket in a river brings down Pharaoh. An ant's warning stops an army. A bird's report converts a queen. The pattern is consistent, and the sūrah named after the ant is the sūrah in which the pattern is most visible. The reader's inference is meant to be practical: the significance of what one carries is not determined by one's size, and the smallest available instrument, correctly used, can be the one through which the largest thing happens.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:23

> **Verily, I found a woman ruling over them, and she has been given of all things, and hers is a mighty throne!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Report Begins With the Fact That Struck the Reporter**

*Innī wajadtu imraʾatan tamlikuhum wa ūtiyat min kulli shayʾin wa lahā ʿarshun ʿaẓīm* — "Verily I found a woman ruling over them, and she has been given of all things, and hers is a mighty throne."

The hoopoe's report has three clauses, and their order is significant. The first is political and unprecedented: a woman rules them. The second is economic: she has been given of all things. The third is symbolic: she has a mighty throne. The report moves from *who governs*, to *what she has*, to *what represents her power* — and the sūrah will spend the next twenty-two verses reversing the order, ending with the throne at Solomon's feet (v. 40), the wealth refused (v. 36), and the ruler submitting (v. 44).

**Imraʾatan Tamlikuhum: A Woman Possessing Sovereignty**

The verb *tamlikuhum* is from *m-l-k*, to own, to possess, to hold sovereignty over. The Form IV and the construct with the object pronoun give the sense of holding dominion *over them* — she is their sovereign, not their manager or regent. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Rāzī all note that the hoopoe reports the fact without comment and that the Qur'an reports the hoopoe's report without comment.

This silence is itself the exegetical datum, and it has been discussed for fourteen centuries. The Qur'an does not treat a woman's sovereignty as an anomaly requiring explanation, an offense requiring censure, or a wonder requiring remark. It states it. And it then devotes more narrative space to this woman's deliberation, judgment, and decision than to almost any other individual in the chapter except Solomon.

The classical commentators, writing in societies with their own assumptions, disagreed about the verse's implications. Al-Qurṭubī records the well-known ḥadīth cited in discussions of female political authority, transmitted by al-Bukhārī from Abū Bakrah:

> *"A people will not succeed who make a woman their ruler."* (al-Bukhārī 4425, 7099)

The commentators themselves supply the context that determines the ḥadīth's scope: Abū Bakrah reported that he heard it when news reached the Prophet ﷺ that the Persians had made the daughter of Khosrow their sovereign, and the report was understood as a prediction about that specific polity, which shortly collapsed. The ḥadīth's own wording, in the majority of the tradition's legal reasoning, is read as a statement about a particular case and about the conditions of a particular office, not as a universal disqualification — a reading supported by the Qur'an's own presentation here, in which the woman ruler is portrayed as *more* prudent than her male council (v. 34), *more* consultative than they are (v. 32), and ultimately as a believer whose submission Ibn ʿArabī judges superior to Pharaoh's.

Ibn ʿArabī's observation in the *Fuṣūṣ al-ḥikam*, recorded in the source commentary, is the sharpest statement of the point: the queen's handling of the letter from Solomon demonstrated her superiority and worthiness to rule; and her willingness to embrace true faith when challenged — unlike Pharaoh and other such figures — can be read as constituting a Qur'anic model for beneficent and wise female leadership and governance.

Whatever position a reader takes on the juristic question, the narrative fact of verse 23 is not in dispute: the Qur'an chose to tell a story in which a woman is the sovereign, the strategist, the consulter, the recognizer of truth, and the first in her nation to submit — and in which no male character in her own kingdom does any of those things as well as she does.

**"She Has Been Given of All Things"**

*Wa ūtiyat min kulli shayʾ* — "and she has been given of all things." The verb *ūtiyat* is the passive of *āta*, Form IV from *ʾ-t-y*, to give, to grant, to bestow. The passive voice is significant and the commentators note it: she has *been given*, not she has acquired or achieved. The same idiom appeared in verse 16 of Solomon himself — *wa ūtīnā min kulli shayʾ* — and al-Rāzī explains both as meaning "much" or "abundantly," everything needed for the office held, rather than literally everything in existence.

The repetition of the phrase across the two figures is a deliberate structural device. Solomon and Bilqīs are described in the same words, and the sūrah's whole subsequent argument is about what each of them does with what they have been given. Both were given abundance; both were given it by God; one had already learned to say *faḍl mubīn* (v. 16) and the other is about to learn it (v. 44). The phrase is thus not a description of wealth but the setup of a test — the same test verse 40 will name explicitly: *"to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful."*

**"A Mighty Throne": ʿArsh ʿAẒīm**

*Wa lahā ʿarshun ʿaẓīm* — "and she has a mighty throne." The word *ʿarsh* is one of the Qur'an's great terms: it means a throne, a seat of authority, and — of God — the Throne that encompasses creation. The Qur'an speaks of God's Throne repeatedly:

> *"God — there is no god but He, Lord of the mighty Throne (rabbi'l-ʿarshi'l-ʿaẓīm)."* (Qur'an 9:129; cf. 23:86, 40:15)

> *"He it is Who created the heavens and the earth in six days, and His Throne was upon the water."* (Qur'an 11:7)

> *"And the angels are at its edges, and there bear the Throne of thy Lord above them, on that Day, eight."* (Qur'an 69:17)

> *"And thou seest the angels encircling the Throne, glorifying the praise of their Lord."* (Qur'an 39:75)

Al-Qurṭubī's observation at this verse, recorded in the source commentary, is one of the most illuminating in the whole sūrah: the Queen of Sheba's throne is called *mighty* (v. 23) in relation to the other thrones of the sovereigns of the earth, whereas God's Throne is *Mighty* because it — or its Pedestal (*kursī*) — encompasses the heavens and the earth. The identical phrase *al-ʿarsh al-ʿaẓīm* is used of both, and the difference is one of reference frame, exactly as the Qur'an states at verse 26 of this sūrah, where the hoopoe concludes his report:

> *"God — there is no god but He, Lord of the mighty Throne!"* (Qur'an 27:26)

The hoopoe's report is structured as a comparison. Verse 23 announces a mighty throne; verse 26 announces the Lord of *the* mighty Throne. Between them lies the whole argument of the sūrah about power. A throne is the ultimate symbol of human authority — the physical object before which a nation kneels — and the Qur'an takes that object, describes it as mighty, and then in three verses relativizes it against the Throne of God. Verse 38 will have Solomon ask for Bilqīs's throne to be brought to him; verse 40 will have it standing in his court; verse 41 will have it altered and disguised; verse 42 will have its owner uncertain whether she recognizes her own seat of power. The throne's journey across these verses is the sūrah's most sustained object-lesson in the impermanence of sovereignty.

**ʿAẓīm: The Adjective of Magnitude**

*ʿAẓīm* is from *ʿ-ẓ-m*, magnitude, greatness, immensity — the same root as *ʿaẓm*, bone, the hard structural core of a thing. It is one of the Divine Names (*al-ʿAẓīm*) and one of the Qur'an's most frequent adjectives, used of God's Throne, of the Day of Judgment (*yawmun ʿaẓīm*, 26:135, 30:43), of the Qur'an itself (*wa laqad ātaynāka sabʿan mina'l-mathānī wa'l-Qurʾāna'l-ʿaẓīm*, 15:87), and of God's bounty (*faḍlin ʿaẓīm*, 3:174, 4:113).

That the word is applied to Bilqīs's throne is not an error of scale but a setup. The hoopoe, a bird, is reporting the largest thing it has ever seen, and it uses the largest word it has. Three verses later it will use the same word of God's Throne, and the reader will understand the difference between the two uses — a difference the bird itself understands, since its report ends with an act of worship.

**The Report's Rhetoric: A Bird Describing a Kingdom**

There is a final observation about verse 23 that deserves notice. The whole of the hoopoe's report is delivered in three short clauses, and its effect on the reader is to produce, in a few words, a complete picture of a civilization: its government, its economy, its symbols. This is compressed description of a kind the Qur'an rarely uses elsewhere, and it works because the details chosen are the ones that matter.

The queen. The abundance. The throne. Every reader, in any century, immediately understands what kind of polity is being described — a wealthy, centralized, symbolically elaborate monarchy at the peak of its power, ruling a prosperous land. Nothing further is needed. And when verse 44 arrives, twenty-one verses later, and this same ruler says *"My Lord! Surely I have wronged myself, and I submit with Solomon to God, Lord of the worlds,"* the reader will know exactly what has been surrendered: not a preference, but a throne.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:24

> **I found her and her people prostrating to the sun, apart from God, and Satan has made their actions seem fair unto them, and turned them from the way, such that they are not rightly guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hoopoe Becomes a Theologian**

*Wajadtuhā wa qawmahā yasjudūna li'l-shamsi min dūni Llāh wa zayyana lahumu'l-shayṭānu aʿmālahum fa-ṣaddahum ʿani'l-sabīli fa-hum lā yahtadūn* — "I found her and her people prostrating to the sun, apart from God; and Satan has adorned their deeds for them, and barred them from the way, so that they are not guided."

This is the verse that turns the narrative. Everything before it was political geography: a woman rules, she is wealthy, she has a throne. Verse 24 supplies the fact that makes Solomon act — and it is a religious fact. The hoopoe, a bird, delivers a doctrinal diagnosis of a civilization, using vocabulary the Qur'an itself uses in its most theological passages. Al-Qurṭubī notes the commentators' astonishment: the bird reports not merely what it saw but *why* what it saw is wrong.

**Yasjudūna li'l-Shams: Prostration as the Criterion**

The verb *yasjudūn* is from *s-j-d*, to prostrate — to place the forehead on the ground, the posture of total submission in which the highest part of the body, the seat of reason and pride, is brought to the lowest level. In Islamic law prostration (*sujūd*) is the act by which worship is defined, and the Prophet ﷺ described it in a ḥadīth recorded by Muslim:

> *"The nearest a servant is to his Lord is when he is prostrating, so make much supplication therein."* (Muslim 482, from Abū Hurayrah)

And:

> *"As for bowing, magnify the Lord in it; and as for prostration, be earnest in supplication, for it is worthy of being answered for you."* (Muslim 479, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

Because prostration is the definitive act of worship, its direction is the definitive marker of religion. To prostrate *to* something is to worship it. The hoopoe therefore does not need to describe Sabaʾ's theology; it describes its posture and the object of that posture: they prostrate to the sun.

Sun-worship in pre-Islamic South Arabia is historically attested — the Sabean pantheon included the sun-goddess Dhat-Ḥimāy (or Shams) — and the Qur'an's account aligns with what the inscriptions and the classical geographers report. The commentators note that sun-worship was among the most philosophically respectable forms of paganism, and al-Rāzī develops the point: unlike idolaters who worship stones they carved themselves, sun-worshippers worship an object of genuine grandeur, genuine utility, and genuine regularity — the most powerful visible thing in the sky, on which all agriculture and life depend. Which is precisely what makes it dangerous. The error is not stupid; it is *attractive*, and its attractiveness is the verse's subject.

**Min Dūni Llāh: The Phrase That Defines Shirk**

*Min dūni Llāh* — "apart from God," literally "from other than God" or "below God." The phrase is one of the Qur'an's most frequent and most technical, appearing more than a hundred times, and it is the standard formula for what worship must never be directed to. Its preposition *dūn* means below, other than, short of — and the phrase carries the sense of setting something up in a position that is not God's, whether beside Him or beneath Him.

> *"And they worship, apart from God, that which neither harms them nor profits them, and they say: 'These are our intercessors with God.'"* (Qur'an 10:18)

> *"Say: 'Call upon those you suppose, apart from God; they possess not an atom's weight in the heavens or on the earth.'"* (Qur'an 34:22)

The Qur'an's argument against *shirk* — association — is not that the associated objects do not exist. The sun exists. The idol exists. The argument is that they do not *deserve* prostration, because they do not create, do not provide, do not hear, and do not govern. Verse 25 will make exactly this argument, and the hoopoe will make it — a bird refuting sun-worship.

**Satan as the Agent of Adornment**

*Wa zayyana lahumu'l-shayṭānu aʿmālahum* — "and Satan has adorned their deeds for them." The verb *zayyana* is the identical verb used of God in verse 4 of this sūrah: *zayyannā lahum aʿmālahum*, "We have made their deeds seem fair unto them." Here the agent is named as Satan.

The commentators treat these two verses together, and the reconciliation is the one established at verse 4: the same event has two descriptions, at two levels of causation. Satan does the adorning — he is the immediate, active agent, whispering, suggesting, presenting evil in attractive form. God permits it and, where the person has already chosen it, confirms it — so that the Qur'an can attribute the same phenomenon to God's will at the level of ultimate causation and to Satan at the level of proximate agency. The Qur'an states both attributions in different places without contradiction, and it also states the third form, the passive with no agent named:

> *"The evil of their deeds is made to seem fair unto them."* (Qur'an 9:37)

> *"And he said: 'Verily I took an idol apart from You — no, rather Satan has deceived me'"* — cf. the Devil's own disavowal: *"Verily I disbelieved in your associating me with God before"* (Qur'an 14:22)

Satan's method is described in the Qur'an with unusual specificity, and it is worth collecting because verse 24 assumes it:

> *"He said: 'My Lord, since Thou hast made me err, I shall surely adorn [evil] for them on the earth, and I shall surely lead them all astray, save Thy servants among them, the sincere ones.'"* (Qur'an 15:39–40)

> *"Then I shall come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right hands and from their left hands, and Thou wilt not find most of them grateful."* (Qur'an 7:17)

The two descriptions of Satan's work — *adornment* and *encirclement* — are complementary. Adornment changes how a thing looks; encirclement ensures that there is no direction from which the person can see it otherwise. Verse 24 uses the first and adds the second: Satan adorned their deeds *and barred them from the way* (*fa-ṣaddahum ʿani'l-sabīl*).

**Fa-Ṣaddahum ʿAni'l-Sabīl: The Barrier**

The verb *ṣadda* is from *ṣ-d-d*, to turn away, to bar, to obstruct — Form I transitive, "he barred them." The Qur'an uses this verb of Satan's characteristic operation:

> *"And Satan barred them from the way, though they were people of perception."* (Qur'an 29:38)

> *"Verily Satan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy. He calls his party only to be companions of the Blaze."* (Qur'an 35:6)

> *"Satan made their deeds seem fair unto them and barred them from the way, though they were endowed with keen sight."* (Qur'an 29:38)

The phrase in 29:38 is almost word-for-word identical to the hoopoe's report — *zayyana lahumu'l-shayṭānu aʿmālahum fa-ṣaddahum ʿani'l-sabīl* — and it adds the qualification that these people were *mustabṣirīn*, endowed with insight and perception. That addition is the point: barring from the way is not imposed on the blind. It is imposed on people who could see, and who were prevented not by removing their sight but by removing their access to the road.

*Sabīl* — way, road, path — is the Qur'an's central spatial metaphor for religion. It appears in *sabīl Allāh* (the way of God), in *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm* (the straight path) of the Fātiḥah, and in the two ways of 76:3: *"Verily We guided him to the way, be he grateful or ungrateful."* Religion in the Qur'an is consistently conceived as locomotion: there is a road, there is a destination, there are obstacles, and there are those who bar the road. The hoopoe's diagnosis of Sabaʾ uses this vocabulary with complete fluency, and the diagnosis is threefold: they were shown something bad as good (adornment); they were prevented from reaching the road (barrier); and the result is that they do not arrive (non-guidance).

**"So They Are Not Guided": The Present Tense of a Settled State**

*Fa-hum lā yahtadūn* — "so they are not guided." The verb *yahtadūn* is Form VIII from *h-d-y*, to be guided, to find the way, to guide oneself. The present tense with the negative *lā* in Arabic describes a habitual or continuing state, and the commentators note the difference between this and verse 41 of the same sūrah, where Solomon asks whether the queen is one of those *alladhīna lā yahtadūn* — "who are not guided." Both use the same phrase, and both describe a condition rather than an event.

The clause is the third term of the hoopoe's diagnosis and the consequence of the first two. Adornment distorts appraisal; barring removes access; and the result is that guidance does not occur. The Qur'an's account of unbelief is thus never simply "they chose wrongly." It is a process, with stages, each of which follows from the last, and each of which has a named agent. That a bird articulates this process in one verse is one of the sūrah's most striking features — and it is a feature the commentators did not explain away.

**The Modern Form of Sun-Worship**

The verse's application to a contemporary reader does not require straining. Nobody in a modern city prostrates to the sun. But the structure of the error is intact and widely reproduced, and the structure is: prostration directed at something visible, powerful, useful, and regular — something that genuinely does govern large portions of one's life — which is not God.

The candidates are obvious and require no enumeration, though their common feature does: each is *seen*, each is *measurable*, each delivers *predictable benefits*, and each therefore seems, to the person prostrating, to be the most reasonable object of devotion available. Money is visible and reliable. Status is visible and reliable. Approval is visible and reliable. Health is visible and reliable. Nation is visible. Career is visible. The sun was the most rational object of worship available to a pre-modern agricultural civilization — more reliable than any idol, more powerful than any king, more universally beneficial than any ancestor — and it was wrong, not because it lacked those qualities but because it did not have the quality that matters: it did not create itself, and it will be extinguished.

> *"And among His signs are the night and the day, and the sun and the moon. Do not prostrate to the sun or the moon, but prostrate to God, Who created them, if it is Him you worship."* (Qur'an 41:37)

Verse 41:37 is the Qur'an's direct legislation on the matter, and its argument is exactly the hoopoe's: these are *signs*, not objects; they point, and pointing is what a sign does. The error of Sabaʾ was not that they admired the sun too much. It was that they stopped at the sign and did not follow it. And verse 25 shows that the hoopoe knows this, because its next words do precisely what the sun should have done: they point past it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:25

> **Will they not prostrate unto God, Who brings forth what is hidden in the heavens and on the earth, and Who knows what you hide and what you disclose?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Bird's Theology: A Refutation in One Sentence**

*Allā lā yasjudū li'Llāhi'lladhī yukhriju'l-khabʾa fī'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ wa yaʿlamu mā tukhfūna wa mā tuʿlinūn* — "Will they not prostrate to God, Who brings forth the hidden thing in the heavens and the earth, and Who knows what you hide and what you disclose?"

This verse is one of the most remarkable in the Qur'an for what it puts in whose mouth. A hoopoe — the same bird Solomon threatened to slaughter two verses earlier for being absent without leave — delivers a coherent argument for the exclusive right of God to be worshiped, an argument with a premise, a contrast, and a conclusion.
