Al-Rāzī devotes extended discussion to it, and the commentators generally treat it as evidence of the kind of understanding God gave Solomon's birds: not human reason, but an inspiration (*ilhām*) proper to their state, sufficient for the purpose it serves.

**The Grammatical Question: Allā or Al-Lā**

The source commentary records a textual point of real consequence. The first word can be read in two ways, both accepted.

Read as *allā* — a contraction of *an lā*, "that not" — the verse continues from verse 24 as a purpose or result clause: *"and Satan turned them from the way, so that they are not guided, and so that they do not prostrate to God."* On this reading the sentence is a further description of what Satan's barring accomplished: he prevented them from prostrating to God.

Read as *alā* — the interrogative particle of reproach and incitement, "will they not? is it not the case that?" — the verse is an exclamation: *"Will they not prostrate to God...?"* The particle *alā* in Arabic opens a rhetorical question that expects agreement and often carries reproach, as in *"alā bi-dhikri Llāhi taṭmaʾinnu'l-qulūb"* — "is it not by the remembrance of God that hearts find rest?" (13:28).

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note both readings are sound. The choice affects who is speaking and to what end. On the first reading the clause belongs to the hoopoe's continuous report and describes the state of Sabaʾ. On the second it is either the hoopoe's indignant question or — as some commentators suggest — an interpolation of the divine voice, and it becomes an appeal.

The second reading is more powerful and is followed by most translations, because it makes the verse a summons rather than a description. And a summons is what the narrative needs at this point: the report has established the problem, and the reader is being asked the question that Sabaʾ failed to answer.

**Yukhriju'l-Khabʾ: The One Who Brings Out the Hidden**

The phrase *alladhī yukhriju'l-khabʾa fī'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — "Who brings forth the hidden thing in the heavens and the earth" — is the argument's premise, and the word *khabʾ* is unusual.

*Khabʾ* is a verbal noun from *kh-b-ʾ*, to hide, to conceal, to store away — the same root as *khazīnah*, a storehouse, and *makhbaʾ*, a hiding place. The *khabʾ* is "the hidden thing," the concealed content. Al-Ṭabarī records the interpretations: rain (*ghayth*), which is hidden in the clouds and brought out; the fruits and plants of the earth, hidden in seeds and brought out; the treasures and creatures of the heavens and earth known only to God; and mysteries generally, as al-Qurṭubī has it.

All of these readings converge on a single proposition: God is the One who produces what is not presently available. He brings out rain from a cloud that looked empty; he brings out a tree from a seed that looked inert; he brings out a person from a womb that concealed him; he brings out the Day from a future that has not arrived. The Qur'an makes this its own repeated argument:

> *"And He it is Who sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy, until, when they have carried a heavy cloud, We drive it unto a dead land, and therewith send down water, and bring forth therewith every kind of fruit. Even so We bring forth the dead, that haply you may remember."* (Qur'an 7:57)

> *"Verily God is the Cleaver of the grain and the date-stone; He brings forth the living from the dead, and brings forth the dead from the living. That is God — so how are you turned away?"* (Qur'an 6:95)

> *"And among His signs is that thou seest the earth lowly, and when We send down water upon it, it stirs and swells. Verily He Who gives it life is the Giver of life to the dead. Truly He has power over all things."* (Qur'an 41:39)

**The Argument Against the Sun, Precisely Stated**

The premise is aimed at the object of Sabaʾ's prostration with surgical accuracy, and the logic deserves to be laid out.

The sun is the most *visible* thing in the sky. It cannot be hidden; it cannot be missed; it is the paradigm of the manifest. And yet the sun produces nothing that is not already produced. It does not bring rain out of a cloud; it does not bring a plant out of a seed; it does not bring a child out of a womb; it does not know what is in anyone's heart. It rises and sets with a regularity that is not its own accomplishment, and it does exactly what it did yesterday.

God, by contrast, is the One who brings out what is hidden — the invisible becomes visible, the stored becomes available, the dead becomes living, the secret becomes known. That is a power the sun does not have.

Therefore: prostrate to the One who brings forth the hidden, not to the thing that is merely the most conspicuous. The argument is not that the sun is insignificant; it is that visibility is not divinity. And it is an argument against the most persistent error in human religious history — the identification of God with the largest visible object in the environment.

**"Who Knows What You Hide and What You Disclose"**

*Wa yaʿlamu mā tukhfūna wa mā tuʿlinūn* — "and He knows what you hide and what you disclose." The pair *tukhfūna* / *tuʿlinūn* is from *kh-f-y*, to hide, and *ʿ-l-n*, to be open, public, manifest — the same root as *ʿalāniyah*, publicly, and *iʿlān*, declaration. The Qur'an uses this pair constantly as a comprehensive formula for the totality of a person's inner and outer life:

> *"Whether you conceal your speech or disclose it — verily He knows what is in the breasts."* (Qur'an 67:13)

> *"And your Lord knows what their breasts conceal and what they disclose."* (Qur'an 28:69 — quoted again at verse 74 of this sūrah)

> *"Say: 'Whether you hide what is in your breasts or disclose it, God knows it.'"* (Qur'an 3:29)

The second half of the hoopoe's argument completes the first, and the completion is deliberate. The first half establishes God's power over the hidden *in the world* — rain, seeds, treasures, the future. The second establishes God's knowledge of the hidden *in the person* — the concealed thought, the unspoken intention, the private act.

Together the two halves answer the deepest reason human beings worship visible things: because visible things can be seen, and being seen is what makes them socially powerful. The sun is worshiped in public; the idol is worshiped in the marketplace; the prostration is performed before witnesses. The hoopoe's argument removes the entire basis of the practice. There is a Witness who sees what no public can see — what you hide — and the only prostration that reaches Him is the one performed in the private dark, where there is no audience to impress.

This is why the Qur'an's definition of the believer at verse 3 of this sūrah ended with certainty of the Hereafter, and why verse 74 will return to God's knowledge of what breasts conceal. The whole of Islamic worship is oriented toward an unseen Object who knows the unseen interior. Sabaʾ's worship was oriented toward a seen object that knew nothing. The contrast is not between two gods; it is between two directions of attention.

**The Prostration of Recitation at This Verse**

Verse 26, which follows immediately, is one of the fifteen places in the Qur'an at which the tradition prescribes a prostration of recitation (*sajdat al-tilāwah*). Al-Qurṭubī notes this at verse 26, and the practice is established in the Ṣaḥīḥs:

> *"Ibn ʿUmar said: The Prophet ﷺ would recite to us a sūrah containing a prostration, and he would prostrate, and we would prostrate with him, until some of us could not find room for his forehead."* (al-Bukhārī 1075; Muslim 575)

And from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ said: 'When the son of Adam recites the verse of prostration and prostrates, Satan withdraws weeping, saying: Woe to him — he was commanded to prostrate and he prostrated, so his is the Garden; and I was commanded to prostrate and I refused, so mine is the Fire.'"* (Muslim 81)

The placement of a prostration of recitation at this point in al-Naml is exact. The verse immediately preceding it reports that a nation prostrated to the sun instead of God. The verse itself declares *"God — there is no god but He, Lord of the mighty Throne."* And the reader who recites it aloud, in the tradition's practice, responds by putting his own forehead on the ground — performing, in his own body, the very act Sabaʾ misdirected. The recitation becomes a correction.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:26

> **God, there is no god but He, Lord of the mighty Throne!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hoopoe's Shahādah**

*Allāhu lā ilāha illā Huwa rabbi'l-ʿarshi'l-ʿaẓīm* — "God — there is no god but He, Lord of the mighty Throne."

Four words of Arabic, and they contain the complete formula of Islamic faith. The verse closes the hoopoe's report not with a recommendation or an observation but with a declaration of God's oneness, and it does so in the exact grammatical shape of the *shahādah* that every Muslim pronounces to enter Islam and repeats throughout his life.

*Allāh* — the proper Name, standing alone in the nominative, which Arabic grammarians analyze either as an initial announcement ("God!") or as the subject of the following clause. *Lā ilāha illā Huwa* — "no god but He," the formula of *tawḥīd*, negation followed by exception, which the tradition calls the two halves of the testimony: first the removal of every false object of worship, then the establishment of the true one. *Rabb al-ʿarsh al-ʿaẓīm* — "Lord of the mighty Throne," the description that locates the One confessed in His sovereignty.

The Qur'an uses this exact formula of itself and commands it of its bearer:

> *"God — there is no god but He, Lord of the mighty Throne."* (Qur'an 27:26)

> *"He — there is no god but He, Lord of the mighty Throne."* (Qur'an 23:86)

> *"God — there is no god but He; unto Him belong the most beautiful Names."* (Qur'an 20:8)

> *"Your God is one God; there is no god but He, the Compassionate, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 2:163)

And the Prophet ﷺ described the weight of the formula in the ḥadīth recorded by al-Tirmidhī and Aḥmad:

> *"The best remembrance is 'Lā ilāha illa Llāh,' and the best supplication is 'al-ḥamdu li-Llāh.'"* (al-Tirmidhī 3383; Ibn Mājah 3800, graded ḥasan)

And in the report on the scale, recorded by al-Tirmidhī and Aḥmad from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr:

> *"The scales will be set on the Day of Resurrection, and a man will be brought and his deeds placed in the scale, and they will be light. Then a card (biṭāqah) will be brought forth, upon which is 'Lā ilāha illa Llāh, Muḥammadun rasūlu Llāh,' and it will be placed on the other side, and it will outweigh them."* (al-Tirmidhī 2639; Ibn Mājah 4300, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

That a bird pronounces the *shahādah* in the Qur'an is not a curiosity to be explained away; it is a statement about the scope of the formula. *Lā ilāha illa Llāh* is not a human invention, a tribal slogan, or an Islamic particularity. It is the truth about reality, which creatures recognize according to their capacity and which this sūrah shows a bird articulating more clearly than a nation of human beings did.

**Rabb: The Name of Sustaining Lordship**

*Rabb* is one of the Qur'an's most frequent terms for God and one of its most carefully defined. The root *r-b-b* carries the senses of ownership, mastery, nurturing, and bringing something to its completion by degrees: a *rabb* is one who owns, who maintains, who educates, who brings to maturity. Arabic uses *rabba'l-bayt* for the master of a house and *rabb al-māl* for the owner of capital; and *tarbiyah* — upbringing, cultivation — is from the same root.

Applied to God, *Rabb* means the One who owns all things, sustains all things, and brings all things to their appointed completion. The Qur'an's use of the term is systematic: it is the Name most often on the tongues of its prophets in supplication — *rabbanā*, *rabbi* — and it is the Name most often invoked in argument with those who deny God, because lordship is the aspect of divinity that even the pagans of Makkah acknowledged:

> *"And if thou askest them: 'Who created the heavens and the earth?' they will surely say: 'God.'"* (Qur'an 39:38)

> *"And most of them believe not in God without associating partners with Him."* (Qur'an 12:106)

The distinction the tradition drew from such verses is between *rubūbiyyah* — lordship, the acknowledgment that God creates, provides, and governs — and *ulūhiyyah* — divinity, the acknowledgment that God alone is to be worshiped. Ibn Taymiyyah made this distinction the organizing principle of his theology: the pagan Arabs accepted *rubūbiyyah* and denied *ulūhiyyah*; the prophets' mission was to move from the first to the second. Sabaʾ's error, on this analysis, was exactly that: they knew something governed the world, and they located the governance in the sun.

**Al-ʿArsh al-ʿAẓīm: The Throne as the Limit of Created Magnitude**

*Rabb al-ʿarsh al-ʿaẓīm* — "Lord of the mighty Throne." Al-Qurṭubī's observation at verse 23 is completed here. Bilqīs's throne was called mighty *in relation to the thrones of earthly sovereigns*; God's Throne is mighty because it — or its Pedestal, the *kursī* — encompasses the heavens and the earth. The Qur'an describes the Pedestal in the verse the tradition calls the greatest verse in the Book:

> *"God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Ever-Watchful. Neither slumber overtakes Him nor sleep. Unto Him belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth. Who is there that can intercede with Him save by His leave? He knows what is before them and what is behind them, and they encompass nothing of His Knowledge save what He wills. His Pedestal (kursī) extends over the heavens and the earth, and the preservation of both burdens Him not. And He is the Exalted, the Great."* (Qur'an 2:255)

And of the Throne itself:

> *"And the angels bear the Throne of thy Lord above them on that Day, eight."* (Qur'an 69:17)

> *"And We created the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them in six days, and no fatigue touched Us."* (Qur'an 50:38)

> *"It is He Who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then established Himself upon the Throne (istawā ʿalā'l-ʿarsh)."* (Qur'an 57:4; 7:54; 10:3; 13:2; 20:5; 25:59; 32:4)

The doctrine of the Throne in Sunni creed follows the method stated at verse 8 of this sūrah and developed by Ibn Taymiyyah and the salaf generally: the Throne is affirmed as a real creation, the greatest of created things, and God's *istiwāʾ* upon it is affirmed as He described it without asking *how* (*bilā kayf*), without likening it to the sitting of a creature (*tashbīh*), and without emptying the word of meaning (*taʿṭīl*). The Throne is not God's container; God is not in need of it; it is the created limit of magnitude, and it is called *ʿaẓīm* because nothing created exceeds it.

**The Rhetorical Function of the Throne in the Hoopoe's Report**

The reason the bird ends with the Throne is now visible, and it is the reason the sūrah spends the next twenty verses on Bilqīs's throne. The report began by describing a mighty throne in Sabaʾ (v. 23); it ends by declaring the Lord of *the* mighty Throne. Between the two, the sun has been dethroned.

The sequence is a complete argument in the sūrah's own idiom. Sabaʾ prostrates to the sun. The sun is a created thing. The One who brings out what is hidden in the heavens and earth created the sun and knows what is in your heart. Therefore prostrate to Him. And He is described by the largest thing there is — the Throne — so that the largest thing Sabaʾ has, Bilqīs's throne, is immediately placed inside a larger order, as a possession of the Lord of *the* Throne.

By the time verse 38 arrives, and Solomon asks *"which of you will bring me her throne?"*, the reader has already been given the interpretive key. The throne that will be transported across Arabia in the blink of an eye is a small object in a universe whose Throne is borne by eight angels. Its transport is not a display of Solomon's power; it is a demonstration of the scale in which Solomon's power exists.

**A Bird, a Nation, and the Order of Understanding**

The final observation on this verse is the one the sūrah most wants made. A bird has just corrected the religion of a kingdom. The kingdom had wealth, an army, a throne, a queen of recognized intelligence, counselors of recognized strength, and an astronomical cult of considerable antiquity and philosophical respectability. The bird had none of these things, had been absent without leave that morning, and had been threatened with slaughter.

The Qur'an does not present this as absurd. It presents it as true — and the truth it presents is that the recognition of God is not a function of intelligence, resources, education, or rank. It is a function of *direction*: whether one follows the signs where they point. Sabaʾ looked at the sun and stopped. The hoopoe looked at the sun, and at the hidden things the sun does not produce, and at the secret thoughts the sun does not know, and it followed the evidence to its source.

That is the whole difference, and the verse that states it is spoken by the smallest character in the story.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:27

> **He said, "We shall see if you have spoken the truth or if you are among the liars."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response of a Ruler to a Subordinate's Claim**

*Qāla sananẓuru a-ṣadaqta am kunta mina'l-kādhibīn* — "He said: 'We shall see whether you have spoken the truth, or whether you are among the liars.'"

Solomon's reply is remarkable for what it does not contain. The hoopoe has made an extraordinary claim: that a king's bird, absent without leave, has comprehended knowledge the prophet-king himself lacks (v. 22), about a distant kingdom, its religion, its queen, and its throne. Solomon does not accept the claim on the bird's word. He does not reject it on the bird's record. He does not punish it for the absence, which was the subject of the threat in verse 21. He suspends judgment and orders verification.

*Sananẓur* — "we shall see" — is from *n-ẓ-r*, to look, to see, to examine, to consider, to wait for. The verb has both senses in Arabic: physical looking and deliberate consideration, and it also carries the sense of *intizār*, awaiting an outcome. The *sīn* of the future tense marks a near-future action with certainty: "we shall certainly see." Solomon is not dismissing the bird; he is placing its claim under examination and stating that the examination will happen.

**Two Verbs, Two Categories, and a Deliberate Asymmetry**

The two alternatives in the verse are not grammatically parallel, and the commentators noted the asymmetry as meaningful.

*A-ṣadaqta* — "have you spoken the truth?" — is the perfect tense of *ṣadaqa*, to be truthful, to tell the truth. The perfect tense describes a completed act: did you, on this occasion, speak truthfully?

*Am kunta mina'l-kādhibīn* — "or were you among the liars?" — is the imperfect *kunta* followed by the partitive construction *min al-kādhibīn*, "among the liars." The construction describes not a single act but *membership in a class*: are you one of the people whose character is lying?

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī both draw out the difference. Solomon asks whether the bird told the truth *this time*, but whether it belongs to the category of liars *generally*. The two questions are not the same, because a truthful person can be wrong and a liar can accidentally say something true. What Solomon is testing is not only the report's accuracy but the reporter's character — and the phrasing shows that he understands the distinction.

The principle is foundational in the Islamic sciences of testimony and hadith criticism, both of which evaluate a report by examining its transmitter's reliability (*ʿadālah*) and precision (*ḍabṭ*) as well as the report's content. Al-Shāfiʿī codified the requirement of trustworthy transmission, and the scholars of ḥadīth built an entire discipline on the proposition that a statement's truth-value cannot be assessed apart from the character of the one stating it. Solomon's sentence in verse 27 is the Qur'an's earliest illustration of the method.

**Kādhibīn: The Active Participle and the Habitual Liar**

*Kādhib* is the active participle of *kadhaba*, to lie. The active participle in Arabic describes a settled quality or ongoing state rather than a single event — a *kādhib* is one whose lying is characteristic. The Qur'an uses the same construction of its opponents:

> *"Nay, but they denied the truth when it came unto them, so they are in a matter confused."* (Qur'an 50:5)

> *"And they denied them — though their souls were convinced of them — wrongfully and exultantly."* (Qur'an 27:14)

And it uses the plural *al-kādhibīn* in exactly this class sense:

> *"Then let us invoke the curse of God upon the liars (al-kādhibīn)."* (Qur'an 3:61)

> *"Woe on that Day to those who deny — those who deny the Day of Judgment. And none denies it save every sinful transgressor."* (Qur'an 83:10–12)

The verse at 3:61 is the *mubāhalah* — the mutual imprecation — in which the Prophet ﷺ was commanded to invite the Christians of Najrān to a joint invocation of God's curse upon whichever party was lying. It is the Qur'an's strongest statement that truth-claims have consequences and that the parties to a dispute can be called to stake themselves on their sincerity.

Solomon's question to the hoopoe operates in the same register at a smaller scale: your report will be tested, and if it fails, the failure will not be recorded as a mistake but as evidence that you belong to a category. That is a serious standard, and it is the standard the Qur'an applies to its own claims throughout — most explicitly at verse 64 of this sūrah (*"Bring your proof, if you are truthful"*) and at 4:82 (*"Had it been from other than God, they would have found in it much discrepancy"*).

**The Leader Who Accepts Excuses**

Al-Qurṭubī draws a governance lesson from the verse that the source commentary records: the exchange shows that a leader should accept the legitimate excuses of his followers. Solomon had threatened severe punishment or slaughter "unless he brings me a clear warrant" (v. 21). The hoopoe has now brought something that may be a clear warrant. Solomon's response is not to execute the threat but to examine the warrant — and al-Qurṭubī cites in this connection the ḥadīth:

> *"None loves excuses (maʿādhīr) more than God. That is why He revealed the Books and sent the messengers."* (al-Bukhārī 7416; Muslim 1499)

The connection is exact. God's own method — revelation sent so that no one can claim ignorance — is a method of *enabling* excuses in the sense of removing false ones and establishing legitimate ones. A just ruler imitates this: he does not want to punish, he wants to know, and he structures his procedure so that the truth has a route to him.

There is a further point about power in the verse. Solomon is absolute monarch over men, jinn, and birds. Nothing compels him to investigate. He could have executed the hoopoe on the spot and no one would have questioned it. The fact that he chooses verification instead — and chooses it in a matter involving a bird — establishes a norm that the Islamic legal tradition would later generalize: authority is not exempted from the duty of inquiry by the possession of power. The stronger the authority, the greater the obligation to verify, because the stronger the authority, the more damaging the unverified act.

**"We Shall See": The Patience Built Into the Sentence**

The phrase *sananẓur* has been read by the commentators as containing an element of waiting. Solomon does not say "prove it now." He says "we shall see" — which allows time for the verification to occur. And the narrative supplies the verification in the most efficient way possible: Solomon sends the hoopoe back with a letter (v. 28), and the queen's own response becomes the evidence.

This is a method of enormous practical value, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly. Rather than demanding immediate proof from a claimant, the wise ruler creates a situation in which the claim will be tested by events. Solomon does not interrogate the bird about Sabaʾ's geography, its army, or its queen's appearance — details a liar could not have known but a truthful bird could not easily demonstrate either. He writes a letter, sends it by the very bird that made the claim, and waits to see what comes back. If the kingdom does not exist, the letter will not be delivered. If the queen does not rule, no queen will answer. If the religion is not as described, no sun-worshiping nation will receive a summons to submit to God.

The test is designed so that the report verifies itself. This is the same method verse 69 will prescribe to the reader — *"Journey upon the earth and behold how the guilty fared in the end"* — and the same method verse 93 will announce as God's own procedure — *"He will show you His signs, and you will know them."* In each case the truth is not argued; it is arranged to appear.

**The Reader's Position: Verifying the Qur'an's Own Claims**

There is a final layer to the verse. Solomon's response to the hoopoe's extraordinary report is a model for the reader's response to the Qur'an's extraordinary reports. The reader is not asked to believe without examination, and he is not permitted to reject without examination either. He is asked to do what Solomon did: hold the claim, design a test, and wait.

The Qur'an proposes its own tests. It invites inspection of its internal consistency (4:82). It invites travel to see the ruins of the destroyed peoples (30:9, 27:69). It invites the production of a rival chapter (2:23, 10:38). It invites attention to the signs in the horizons and in the self (41:53). And it promises, in this sūrah's final verse, that the signs will be shown: *"He will show you His signs, and you will know them"* (27:93).

A reader who approaches the Qur'an with Solomon's posture — neither credulous nor dismissive, prepared to be convinced, willing to wait for evidence — is approaching it in the manner the text itself recommends. And the reader who has already decided, before examination, that the report cannot be true, is in the position of verse 14: rejecting what his soul has become certain of, wrongfully and exultantly.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:28

> **Go with this letter of mine and deliver it unto them. Then turn away from them and observe what they send back."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Document in the Qur'an's Diplomatic History**

*Idhhab bi-kitābī hādhā fa-alqih ilayhim thumma tawalla ʿanhum fa-nẓur mādhā yarjiʿūn* — "Go with this letter of mine, and cast it unto them; then withdraw from them, and observe what they return."

Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr note that Solomon is speaking to the hoopoe — the same bird whose report he has just placed under verification, and whose truthfulness he has not yet confirmed. The commission is extraordinary in itself: a prophet-king entrusts his correspondence to a subordinate whose claim he is testing, and the subordinate becomes the ambassador. Al-Qurṭubī observes that the choice demonstrates the principle that a leader should accept legitimate excuses and restore a subordinate to service once a warrant has been offered; al-Rāzī adds that sending the very bird that made the claim is the most efficient possible design of verification, since a fabricator cannot deliver a letter to a place that does not exist.

**Kitāb: The Word for a Written Document**

The word *kitāb* is from *k-t-b*, to write, to inscribe, to prescribe — the same root that gives *kitābah*, writing, *kātib*, scribe, *maktūb*, written and decreed, and *kutub*, books. The Qur'an uses *kitāb* of the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms, the Preserved Tablet, the record of deeds, and the Qur'an itself.

Its use here for a diplomatic letter is significant and has a documented historical parallel. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record the letter of the Prophet ﷺ to Heraclius, and the report includes the letter's opening in the exact formula of verse 30:

> *"Ibn ʿAbbās reported that Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb told him: Heraclius sent for them while they were in Jerusalem, and he asked for the letter of the Messenger of God ﷺ. It was read, and it said: 'In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful. From Muḥammad, servant of God and His Messenger, to Heraclius, great one of the Romans: peace be upon whoever follows the guidance. To proceed: I invite you with the invitation of Islam...'"* (al-Bukhārī 7; Muslim 1773)

The formula *Bismi Llāhi'l-Raḥmāni'l-Raḥīm* at the head of a prophetic letter to a foreign sovereign is precisely what verse 30 will report of Solomon's letter to Bilqīs. Muslim also records from Anas ibn Mālik the detail about the seal:

> *"When the Prophet ﷺ intended to write to the non-Arab rulers, it was said to him: 'They will not read a letter unless it is sealed.' So he took a ring of silver — as though I am looking at its whiteness in his hand — and engraved upon it: 'Muḥammad, Messenger of God.'"* (al-Bukhārī 65; Muslim 2092)

The parallel is exact enough that the commentators treat Solomon's letter as the precedent for the Prophet's ﷺ practice, and the sūrah's report as the divine authorization of written diplomacy in Islam.

**Fa-Alqih Ilayhim: "Cast It Unto Them"**

The verb *alqih* is from *l-q-y*, the same root discussed at verse 6 — to meet, to encounter, to cast, to deliver. The Qur'an uses *alqā* of God's casting of the revelation into a heart (26:194) and of a person's throwing down an object (20:19, where Moses is told *alqi ʿaṣāk*, "cast thy staff"). Its use here for the delivery of a letter carries the sense of placing it into their hands directly, without mediation, without reading it aloud first, without negotiation.

The commentators note the implication: the letter is delivered as a document, complete and unaltered, and its reception is left to the recipients. This is the method of prophetic communication generally — the message is delivered whole, and the hearer is then responsible for his response. The Qur'an describes it in exactly these terms:

> *"And if they turn away, then thy duty is only to convey; and God sees His servants."* (Qur'an 3:20)

> *"So remind; thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a taskmaster over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

> *"Upon the Messenger is only the conveyance."* (Qur'an 5:99)

**Thumma Tawalla ʿAnhum: "Then Withdraw From Them"**

*Tawallā* is Form V from *w-l-y*, to turn, to face, to be near, to take as protector — the same root as *walī*, guardian and friend, and *tawallā*, to turn away. Form V here means "to turn oneself away, to withdraw." The command is to deliver the letter and then leave.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī discuss the reason, and the discussion illuminates prophetic method. Withdrawal serves several functions at once. It removes the ambassador from danger — the letter is a demand for submission, and an envoy who waits for the answer in the court of a proud sovereign is in a vulnerable position. It removes pressure from the recipients — a decision made while a messenger watches is not fully free. And it protects the message from contamination: the envoy who waits may be questioned, cajoled, bribed, or persuaded to modify what he carries.

There is a further wisdom that the commentators draw from the sequence. The withdrawal makes the *response* the evidence. If the envoy stayed and argued, the outcome would be a debate, and the debate's result would depend on the envoy's skill. By withdrawing, Solomon converts the exchange into a test of the recipients' own character: what they do with the letter, alone, is what will reveal them. This is the same method he applied to the hoopoe's claim, and the same method the Qur'an applies to its own readers — deliver the message, withdraw, and observe.

**Fa-Nẓur Mādhā Yarjiʿūn: "Observe What They Return"**

The final clause is *fa-nẓur mādhā yarjiʿūn* — "then look at what they bring back." *Nẓur* is the same verb of verse 27 — *sananẓur*, "we shall see" — now in the imperative. The verb appears twice in two verses, marking the two halves of the verification procedure: Solomon will see whether the bird told the truth (v. 27), and he will see what the recipients return (v. 28). The first *naẓar* concerns the report; the second concerns the response. Together they constitute the complete inquiry.

*Yarjiʿūn* is from *r-j-ʿ*, to return — the same root as *rajʿah*, return, and *marjiʿ*, the place of return. The Qur'an uses this root constantly of the return to God:

> *"Unto God is your return (marjiʿukum) all together, and He will inform you of what you used to do."* (Qur'an 5:105)

> *"Unto Him is the return of you all."* (Qur'an 10:4, 11:4, 29:17)

The word choice is quietly significant. Solomon asks what they will *return* — and the sūrah's whole eschatological framework, which occupies its final third, is about the return of every soul to God with what it has done. The queen's return message (vv. 29–35) is a small-scale model of the return every person will make: with what you have decided, in response to what was delivered to you, after the messenger withdrew.

**The Letter's Content, and What the Verse Does Not Say**

Verse 28 does not quote the letter. Verse 30 will — in the queen's own mouth, as she reads it aloud to her council. The Qur'an's method here is deliberate and instructive: the content of a prophetic message is given not as a document but as a *reception*. The reader encounters Solomon's words first through Bilqīs's ears, and in the act of her reading them.

This has an effect on the reader that a direct quotation would not. We do not overhear Solomon dictating; we watch a sovereign, in her own court, before her own advisers, open a letter and read it. The drama is in her face and in the room. And when the letter's words finally arrive at verse 30 — *"In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful"* — they arrive with the weight of having crossed a continent, been carried by a bird, been handed over in a palace, and been read aloud by a queen who has every reason not to read them.

The verse's practical instruction is correspondingly plain. A message that matters should be delivered whole, delivered and then left, and its outcome observed rather than forced. This is the method of every prophet in the Qur'an, and it is the method the Qur'an prescribes to its own bearer at verse 92 of this very sūrah: *"And [as for] whosoever is astray, say: 'Verily, I am but a warner.'"* Deliver. Withdraw. Observe. The rest belongs to God.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:29

> **She said, "O notables! Truly a noble letter has been delivered unto me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene Shifts to Sheba, and a Queen Reads Aloud**

*Qālat yā ayyuha'l-malaʾu innī ulqiya ilayya kitābun karīm* — "She said: 'O notables! Verily a noble letter has been cast unto me.'"

The verse opens without introduction. There is no "and the hoopoe went, and it delivered the letter, and the queen received it." The narrative cuts directly to the moment of reception, in the queen's own voice, addressing her council. The elision is characteristic of Qur'anic narrative, which the commentators describe as *ḥadhf* — deliberate omission of what the reader can supply — and which produces an effect like that of a camera cutting between scenes: the reader arrives at the palace already in progress.

**Al-Malaʾ: The Word for the Ruling Council**

*Al-malaʾ* is a collective noun from *m-l-ʾ*, to fill — the same root as *malaʾa*, to fill, and *malīʾ*, wealthy or able. A *malaʾ* is a group of people who "fill" the eye with their presence and the room with their standing: the chiefs, the notables, the council of elders, the leading men of a community. Al-Rāghib explains it as the assembly of notables whose presence fills the sight with dignity and whose counsel fills a matter with adequacy.

The Qur'an uses *al-malaʾ* repeatedly and always of the ruling class that confronts a prophet:

> *"And We sent Noah unto his people, and he said: 'O my people, worship God; you have no god other than Him.'" And the notables (al-malaʾ) among his people who disbelieved said: 'We see you not but as a human being like us...'"* (Qur'an 11:25, 27)

> *"The notables of his people who disbelieved said: 'Verily we see you in manifest error.'"* (Qur'an 7:60, 66)

> *"The notables among Pharaoh's people said: 'Verily this is a skilled sorcerer.'"* (Qur'an 7:109)

> *"And the notables among them departed: 'Walk away and be steadfast toward your gods — verily this is a thing desired.'"* (Qur'an 38:6)

> *"And the notables among them said: 'O our people, respond to the caller of God.'"* — cf. 46:31

The word carries, throughout the Qur'an, a consistently negative charge when it denotes the entrenched elite who resist revelation. In verse 29 of al-Naml, however, it appears in a different register: Bilqīs addresses her *malaʾ* and consults them, and they respond with deference rather than conspiracy. The contrast with the *malaʾ* of Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Pharaoh, and Makkah is one of the sūrah's quiet achievements — it presents a ruling council that functions properly.

**Innī Ulqiya Ilayya: The Passive Voice of Reception**

*"Verily there has been cast unto me..."* The verb *ulqiya* is the passive of *alqā*, the same verb Solomon used in the imperative at verse 28: *fa-alqih ilayhim*, "cast it unto them." The Qur'an's narrative echoes its own wording across the scene break: he commanded it to be cast; she reports that it was cast. The reader sees the same event from both ends — the sender's instruction and the receiver's experience — and the correspondence confirms, without the narrator ever stating it, that the hoopoe's mission succeeded.

This is the verification Solomon ordered at verse 27, arriving not as evidence produced but as evidence *reported by the recipient herself*. The kingdom exists. The queen rules. The letter was delivered. Three of the hoopoe's claims are now confirmed in a single clause spoken in a palace in Yemen, and the fourth — the sun-worship — will be confirmed by what the queen does next.

**Kitābun Karīm: Why the Letter Is Called Noble**

The adjective *karīm* is from *k-r-m*, nobility, generosity, honor, preciousness — the same root as *karam*, munificence, *karīm*, noble and generous, and *akram*, more noble. The Qur'an uses it of God (*al-Karīm*, 23:116, 82:6), of the Qur'an itself (*wa innahu la-kitābun ʿazīzun*, cf. *Qurʾānun karīm* at 56:77), of provision (*rizqun karīm*, 8:4, 22:58), of a position (*maqāmin karīm*, 26:58), and of human beings (*inna akramakum ʿinda Llāhi atqākum*, 49:13).

The commentators offer three explanations for Bilqīs's use of the word here, and al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī record all three.

The letter was noble because of its **seal** — a sealed document in the ancient world was a document of authority, and the seal identified the sender as one who possessed an official signet. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the tradition reports the letter was sealed, and that the seal impressed the queen before the contents did. This accords with the Prophet's ﷺ own practice, recorded in the two Ṣaḥīḥs, of sealing his letters to foreign sovereigns because they would not read an unsealed letter (al-Bukhārī 65; Muslim 2092).

The letter was noble because of its **sender** — a king whose reputation had reached Yemen, whose kingdom was known to exceed all others, and whose name alone conferred importance on what he wrote.

The letter was noble because of its **contents** — and this is the reading the verse most strongly supports, since the contents are given in the next verse and they are the *basmalah* and a summons to submission. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both favor this reading: what made the document noble was what it said.

The three readings are not exclusive, and their convergence is instructive. A letter is honored by the authority that sends it, the seal that authenticates it, and the content it carries. The Islamic tradition developed an entire etiquette of correspondence on this basis, and the formula of verse 30 became the standard opening of Muslim letters for fourteen centuries — from the caliphs' chanceries to a grandmother's envelope.

**The Rhetoric of the Announcement: A Sovereign Consults in Public**

The form of the queen's address deserves attention. She does not summon her advisers privately, does not read the letter alone, and does not decide first and announce afterward. She gathers the council and reads the letter to them in its first words: *"O notables! Truly a noble letter has been delivered unto me."*

Al-Rāzī notes the political intelligence in this: by announcing the letter to her council immediately, Bilqīs does three things at once. She shares responsibility for whatever decision follows. She denies anyone the later claim that she concealed information. And she tests the letter's effect on experienced men before committing to a position herself. This is the behavior of a ruler who intends to govern with others rather than over them, and it prepares for verse 32, where she will state the principle explicitly.

The verse thus functions as the sūrah's second portrait of good governance, following Solomon's inspection of the ranks (v. 20) and his suspension of judgment pending verification (v. 27). Al-Naml is unusually interested in how rulers behave, and it presents two — a prophet-king in Palestine and a pagan queen in Yemen — both behaving well, in complementary ways. The comparison will be completed at verse 44, when the queen's governance produces the one outcome Solomon's did not have to produce: a voluntary surrender of the throne.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:30

> **Verily, it is from Solomon and verily it is, 'In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Complete Basmalah Inside a Sūrah**

*Innahu min Sulaymāna wa innahu bismi Llāhi'l-Raḥmāni'l-Raḥīm* — "Verily it is from Solomon, and verily it is: 'In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful.'"

The source commentary records the fact that makes this verse unique in the Qur'an: verse 30 is the only place in the entire Book where the complete *basmalah* — the formula *bismi Llāhi'l-Raḥmāni'l-Raḥīm* — appears *within* a sūrah as part of its narrative content. Every one of the 114 sūrahs but one (Sūrah 9, al-Tawbah) begins with the basmalah, and the scholars have debated whether that opening formula is itself a verse of each sūrah or a separator between them — a debate recorded at length by al-Qurṭubī, al-Nawawī, and Ibn Kathīr. But here, in the body of al-Naml, the formula is quoted as the *content of a letter*, and it is quoted in full.

**The Structure of the Announcement: Two Inna's**

The verse contains two instances of *inna*, the particle of emphasis and substantiation: *innahu min Sulaymān* — "truly it is from Solomon" — and *wa innahu bismi Llāh...* — "and truly it is: in the Name of God..." The doubling has a rhetorical function the commentators noted. Bilqīs announces two things about the document, and each is separately emphasized: its *origin* and its *content*.

The origin first: it is from Solomon. Before the council hears a word of the letter, they hear who sent it — and in the ancient Near East, the sender of a letter determined its weight more than its wording. A letter from a king of Solomon's reputation arrives with its meaning already half-delivered.

Then the content: it begins in the Name of God. And the council hears, in the first words of a foreign sovereign's correspondence, an invocation of the One God by His two Names of mercy. The sequence — sender, then Name — is the sequence of every Muslim letter ever written, and the Islamic chancery tradition took it directly from this verse. Al-Bukhārī's report of the Prophet's ﷺ letter to Heraclius preserves the identical shape: *"In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful. From Muḥammad, servant of God and His Messenger, to Heraclius..."* (al-Bukhārī 7; Muslim 1773).

**Raḥmān and Raḥīm: The Two Names of Mercy**

The two Divine Names in the basmalah are both from the root *r-ḥ-m*, whose concrete meaning is the womb — *raḥim*, the uterus — and whose derived meanings are tenderness, kinship, compassion, and unearned care. Arabic builds the entire semantic field of mercy on the image of the mother's relation to what is in her womb: a care that precedes any act of the cared-for, that is not earned, that is not withdrawable at will, and that sustains life from within.

*Al-Raḥmān* is the intensive form (*faʿlān*), which in Arabic morphology denotes fullness, intensity, and completeness — the pattern of *ghaḍbān* (full of anger), *ʿaṭshān* (full of thirst), *rayyān* (full of moisture). *Al-Raḥmān* therefore means the One whose mercy is total and all-encompassing. *Al-Raḥīm* is the form denoting permanence, repetition, and actualization (*faʿīl*) — the One whose mercy is continually enacted, repeatedly bestowed.

The classical distinction, articulated by Ibn al-Qayyim and before him by al-Khaṭṭābī and Ibn Jarīr, holds that *al-Raḥmān* denotes the quality of mercy as an attribute of the Essence — vast, comprehensive, and exclusively predicable of God (Arabic never applies *raḥmān* to a human being), while *al-Raḥīm* denotes mercy as enacted upon particular recipients, and can be used of creatures: the Qur'an describes the Prophet ﷺ as *"raḥīm"* toward the believers (9:128) and as *"bi'l-muʾminīna raʾūfun raḥīm"* — compassionate, merciful.

Al-Qurṭubī records the well-known formulation: *al-Raḥmān* is mercy in general, extending in this world to believer and disbeliever alike — rain, health, provision, children, air — while *al-Raḥīm* is mercy particular to the believers in the Hereafter. The support for this division is the Qur'an's own:

> *"And My mercy encompasses all things; so I shall ordain it for those who revere [God] and give the alms, and those who believe in Our signs."* (Qur'an 7:156)

> *"Then He established Himself upon the Throne, the Compassionate (al-Raḥmān) — so ask of one aware of Him."* (Qur'an 25:59)

> *"And when there came unto them a Book from God..."* — and: *"The Compassionate taught the Qur'an"* (Qur'an 55:1–2), where the Name opens the sūrah that repeats the question *"which of the favors of your Lord will you two deny?"* thirty-one times.

**Why Solomon's Letter Began With Mercy**

The theological significance of the letter's opening is considerable and has been carefully worked out. Solomon is writing to a nation that worships the sun, to a queen who rules an army, with a demand that will be stated in verse 31 in the plainest terms: *"Do not exalt yourselves against me, but come unto me in submission."* This is not a friendly note. It is a summons backed by the threat stated at verse 37 — *"we shall come upon them with hosts they cannot withstand."*

And yet the letter opens with the two Names of mercy.

Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both note the ordering as characteristic of prophetic communication and of the Qur'an's own method: the invitation precedes the warning, the mercy precedes the power, and the recipient is given the opportunity to respond before being confronted with the consequence. The same ordering governs the structure of the Fātiḥah, in which *"the Compassionate, the Merciful"* precedes *"Master of the Day of Judgment"*; and it governs the Prophet's ﷺ letter to Heraclius, in which the basmalah is followed by *"peace be upon whoever follows the guidance"* before the warning that follows.

There is a further, more practical wisdom. A letter that opens with the Names of mercy cannot be dismissed as a mere ultimatum. It forces the recipient to consider the character of the sender before deciding the character of the message. Bilqīs reads the first line and learns three things: the sender invokes a God, that God is characterized by mercy rather than by war, and the sender considers himself to be acting in that God's Name. Everything in verse 31 will have to be read in that light — and verse 31's demand for submission will therefore be understood not as the arrogance of a stronger king but as the requirement of a Lord whose Name is mercy.

**The Basmalah as the Muslims' Opening**

The Prophet ﷺ established the basmalah as the opening of every significant act, and the ḥadīth literature records this comprehensively. Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī transmit the general formula:

> *"Every important matter that is not begun with 'In the Name of God' is cut off from blessing (abtar)."* (Abū Dāwūd 4840; Ibn Mājah 1894; al-Nasāʾī in al-Kubrā — the wording varies and the scholars discuss its grading)

And the specific applications are enumerated in the Ṣaḥīḥs: before eating (*"When one of you eats, let him mention the Name of God"* — Muslim 2022), before entering the house, before intercourse, before slaughter, before writing, before embarking on a journey, and at the beginning of every sūrah of the Qur'an.

Muslim records the report that fixes the basmalah's place in prayer:

> *"Anas ibn Mālik said: I prayed behind the Messenger of God ﷺ, behind Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and ʿUthmān, and they would begin with 'Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds' — not mentioning 'In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful' at the beginning of the recitation nor at its end."* (Muslim 399)

The jurists' discussion of this report — whether the basmalah is recited aloud, silently, or not at all in prayer, and whether it is a verse of every sūrah or of al-Fātiḥah alone — is one of the longest in Islamic law, and al-Qurṭubī devotes many pages to it in his commentary on the Fātiḥah. The disagreement is itself evidence of the formula's centrality: no phrase in Islam has been more discussed, more recited, or more carefully guarded.

That the Qur'an places the basmalah's only in-sūrah occurrence in the mouth of a Yemeni queen, reading a Hebrew prophet's letter aloud to a council of sun-worshippers, is one of the Book's most economical statements about the universality of its own formula. The basmalah is not an Islamic innovation. It is what prophets have always written at the head of a letter, and it is what a pagan sovereign recognized, on first sight, as noble.

**The Verse's Testimony to Solomon's Self-Description**

One detail in the verse deserves particular notice. Bilqīs announces: *"Verily it is from Solomon"* — and then gives the letter's opening words. She does not report that Solomon described himself with titles. The Prophet's ﷺ letter to Heraclius, by contrast, includes the sender's self-description: *"From Muḥammad, servant of God and His Messenger."*

The omission here is filled by verse 31, which gives the letter's demand, and by the tradition's reports that Solomon's letter read in full: *"In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful. Do not exalt yourselves against me, but come unto me in submission."* The brevity is itself a feature. A letter consisting of an invocation and a single command is the shortest possible diplomatic communication, and its brevity communicates more than length could: the sender does not argue, does not persuade, does not flatter, and does not explain himself. He invokes God and states the requirement. Ibn Kathīr observes that this is the manner of one whose authority he does not need to establish, and the council's reaction in verse 32 — a request for guidance rather than an insult — confirms that the letter was received in the spirit it was written.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:31

> **Do not exalt yourselves against me, but come unto me in submission.'"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Whole of Solomon's Demand in Two Clauses**

*Allā taʿlū ʿalayya waʾtūnī muslimīn* — "Do not exalt yourselves over me, and come unto me as submitters."

The letter's body consists of a prohibition and a command, and the commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr — have analyzed both.

**Allā Taʿlū ʿAlayya: The Prohibition of Self-Elevation**

The verb *taʿlū* is from *ʿ-l-w*, height, rising, being above — the same root as *ʿuluwwan* in verse 14, where Pharaoh's people rejected the signs *"wrongfully and exultantly."* The word family is the Qur'an's designated vocabulary for the specific sin that destroys human beings: not ignorance, not weakness, not appetite, but *self-elevation*.

The Qur'an uses the root consistently and with a consistent object of condemnation:

> *"Verily Pharaoh exalted himself (ʿalā) in the land and made its people into factions."* (Qur'an 28:4)

> *"That is because they used to exalt themselves (yastaʿlūna) in the land without right."* — cf. 28:39, where Pharaoh and his hosts *"exalted themselves in the land without right and thought they would not be returned to Us"*

> *"Verily man transgresses (yaṭghā) because he sees himself as sufficient (istaġhnā)."* (Qur'an 96:6–7)

> *"And do not walk upon the earth exultantly (maraḥan). Truly thou wilt never rend the earth apart, nor wilt thou reach the mountains in height."* (Qur'an 17:37)

> *"And do not turn thy cheek away from people in contempt, nor walk upon the earth exultantly. Verily God loves not any proud boaster."* (Qur'an 31:18)

The prohibition in Solomon's letter is thus not a personal demand that Bilqīs not insult him. It is a demand that she and her people not occupy the position of *ʿuluww* — the elevated, self-sufficient posture that verse 14 identified as the reason Pharaoh's people rejected signs they were certain of. Solomon is not asking for deference to himself; he is forbidding the state of soul that makes truth unacceptable.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both note the preposition *ʿalayya* — "over me, against me" — and explain that the sense is: do not set yourselves above me in defiance, do not confront me with arrogance, do not meet my letter as an equal refusing a superior. Al-Zamakhsharī glosses it as "do not act proudly toward me."

**Waʾtūnī Muslimīn: The Command and Its Object**

*Waʾtūnī muslimīn* — "and come unto me as *muslimīn*." The word is the active participle of Form IV from *s-l-m*, to be safe, to be sound, to submit, to make peace — the same root as *salām*, peace, *salāmah*, safety and soundness, *taslīm*, surrender, and *islām* itself. A *muslim* is one who has submitted, who has made himself sound by surrendering his own claim to autonomy.

Al-Ṭabarī interprets the phrase as coming in a state of submission and obedience to God with faith in His oneness — that is, as Muslims in the religious sense. Others, including al-Rāzī, read it as approaching Solomon in a state of obedience rather than arrogantly or as tyrants, connecting it back to the prohibition in the first clause. Al-Qurṭubī records both readings and notes that they converge: submission to Solomon here is submission to what Solomon represents and carries, which is the command of God.

The Qur'an uses the same word of submission to God as the religion of all the prophets, not as a sectarian label:

> *"And who turns away from the religion of Abraham save him who makes a fool of himself? Verily We chose him in this world, and verily in the Hereafter he is among the righteous. When his Lord said unto him: 'Submit (aslim),' he said: 'I submit (aslamtu) unto the Lord of the worlds.'"* (Qur'an 2:130–131)

> *"And Abraham enjoined it upon his sons, and Jacob: 'O my sons, verily God has chosen for you the religion, so die not except as submitters (muslimūn).'"* (Qur'an 2:132)

> *"And when I inspired the disciples: 'Believe in Me and in My Messenger,' they said: 'We believe, and bear witness that we are submitters (muslimūn).'"* (Qur'an 5:111)

> *"Truly the religion with God is Islam (submission)."* (Qur'an 3:19)

> *"And whoever seeks a religion other than submission (islām), it will not be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers."* (Qur'an 3:85)

Solomon's letter therefore uses the word that the Qur'an reserves for the religion of every prophet, and he uses it in the plural — *muslimīn* — addressing the queen and her nation together. The demand is not that Bilqīs surrender politically. It is that her people become submitters.

**The Sequence: Prohibition Before Command**

The order of the two clauses is not incidental and carries a doctrinal point the commentators drew out. Submission cannot be commanded until elevation is forbidden. A person who is *ʿālī* — raised up in his own estimation — cannot submit, because submission requires the acknowledgment that one is not above something. The letter therefore removes the obstacle before issuing the requirement, in the same sequence the Qur'an itself follows throughout: *lā ilāha* before *illa Llāh*, negation before affirmation, emptying before filling.

This is the grammar of the *shahādah*, and the *shahādah* is the grammar of the letter. *Lā taʿlū ʿalayya* — do not elevate yourselves — corresponds to *lā ilāha*, there is no god. *Waʾtūnī muslimīn* — come to me submitting — corresponds to *illa Llāh*, except God. Solomon's diplomatic correspondence is a *shahādah* in two clauses, and the fact that the Qur'an preserves it in this shape indicates that the tradition understood it as such.

**The Letter's Effectiveness: What Happens Next**

The measure of a communication is its reception, and the sūrah reports the reception of this one in the following verses with unusual precision. The council does not mock the letter. The queen does not tear it up. Her advisers do not propose to execute the messenger — indeed there is no messenger present, since the hoopoe withdrew as commanded (v. 28). Instead, in verse 32, the queen asks for their opinion; in verse 33, they respond with a statement of military capacity and an explicit deferral of the decision to her; in verse 34, she articulates a theory of what conquest does to a city; in verse 35, she proposes a test; and in verse 44, after further evidence, she submits.

Every one of these responses is a serious engagement with the letter's content. Not one of them treats it as the posturing of a foreign king. Al-Rāzī observes that Bilqīs's handling of the letter demonstrated her superiority and worthiness to rule — and Ibn ʿArabī, in the *Fuṣūṣ al-ḥikam*, develops the point into a judgment about her spiritual rank as well.

The reason the letter worked is stated in the letter. It forbade elevation and commanded submission, and it opened with the Names of mercy. A communication that names the obstacle to its own acceptance, in the recipient's own soul, and then removes that obstacle by invoking mercy, has done everything a communication can do. What remains is the recipient's freedom — which is why Solomon withdrew, and why he said *"observe what they send back"* rather than *"compel them to come."*

**Two Clauses and the Whole of Prophecy**

Read at the level the sūrah intends, verse 31 is a summary of every prophetic message in the Qur'an. Every prophet says, in effect: do not exalt yourselves, and submit. Noah says it (7:59), Hūd says it (7:65), Ṣāliḥ says it (7:73 — and this sūrah will quote him at verse 45), Shuʿayb says it (7:85), Moses says it to Pharaoh (20:47), Jesus says it to the Children of Israel (5:72), and Muhammad ﷺ says it to Makkah (41:6). The formula does not vary because the requirement does not vary.

What varies is the response. Pharaoh's is recorded at verse 14 of this sūrah: rejection despite certainty, wrongfully and exultantly. Thamūd's is recorded at verse 48: conspiracy and corruption. Lot's people's is recorded at verse 56: expel the pure. And Bilqīs's is recorded at verse 44: *"My Lord! Surely I have wronged myself, and I submit with Solomon to God, Lord of the worlds."*

The sūrah has arranged its three narratives so that the responses to the identical demand are displayed in full: the tyrant who is certain and refuses, the conspirators who plot, the city that expels its pure, and the queen who surrenders her throne. Solomon's two-clause letter is the demand that produces all four, and verse 31 is therefore the hinge on which the whole middle section of al-Naml turns.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:32

> **She said, "O notables! Give me your opinion in this matter of mine. I am not one to decide on any matter unless you are present."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Qur'an's Textbook Case in Consultation**

*Qālat yā ayyuha'l-malaʾu aftūnī fī amrī mā kuntu qāṭiʿatan amran ḥattā tashhadūn* — "She said: 'O notables! Give me your ruling in this matter of mine. I was never one to cut off a matter until you are present.'"

This verse is the Qur'an's most explicit presentation of *shūrā* — consultation — as a governing practice, and it is presented in the mouth of a woman who is not yet a believer, ruling a nation that worships the sun. The commentators have not hesitated to draw the conclusion: the verse establishes consultation as a principle of good governance independent of the governor's faith, and its practice as a mark of political wisdom wherever it is found.

**Aftūnī: The Verb of the Considered Answer**

*Aftūnī* is the imperative plural of Form IV from *f-t-y*, to give a considered opinion, to issue a ruling, to answer a question requiring judgment. The same root gives *fatwā*, a formal legal opinion, and *muftī*, the one qualified to give one.

The queen's choice of verb is precise. She does not say "tell me what you think" (*qūlū*), which would invite opinions; she says "give me a *fatwā*," which invites a *considered determination* — the answer of people who have weighed the matter and are prepared to be responsible for the conclusion. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the word is used in matters of weight, where a decision must be made and cannot be deferred.

This distinction has direct practical consequence and is one of the reasons the Islamic tradition developed the *fatwā* as a formal institution. An opinion can be offered casually and withdrawn casually. A *fatwā* is issued by someone qualified, on a stated set of facts, with an awareness of the consequences, and the issuer's name is attached to it. A ruler who asks for *iftāʾ* rather than for *raʾy* is asking his advisers to commit themselves.

**Fī Amrī: "In This Matter of Mine"**

The phrase *fī amrī* — "in my affair, my matter" — has been read by the commentators as containing a nuance of responsibility. The queen does not say "in this matter" impersonally; she says "in *my* matter." Al-Rāzī observes that the possessive marks the decision as hers: she is the one who will bear it, and she is telling her council that she understands this.

This is the correct posture for consultation, and its absence is what makes consultation fail in practice. A leader who consults while treating the decision as somebody else's — "the committee decided," "the advisers recommended," "we followed the process" — has not consulted; he has distributed blame. A leader who consults while retaining ownership of the decision — *fī amrī*, in my matter — uses consultation for what it is for: to improve a judgment he will still be the one making.

The Qur'an's own prescription of consultation to the Prophet ﷺ preserves exactly this structure:

> *"And by mercy from God thou wast lenient with them. Hadst thou been rough and hard-hearted, they would surely have dispersed from around thee. So pardon them, and ask forgiveness for them, and consult them in the affair (wa shāwirhum fī'l-amr). Then, when thou hast resolved, put thy trust in God. Verily God loves those who trust."* (Qur'an 3:159)

The sequence in 3:159 is the sequence verse 32 enacts: consult (*shāwirhum*), then resolve (*fa-idhā ʿazamta*), then trust (*fa-tawakkal ʿalā Llāh*). The resolution and the trust are singular — addressed to the Prophet ﷺ alone, not to the group. Consultation is collective; the decision is not.

**Mā Kuntu Qāṭiʿatan Amran Ḥattā Tashhadūn: The Statement of a Habit**

The second half of the verse is a declaration of settled practice: *"I was never one to decide a matter until you are present."* The verb *qāṭiʿah* is the active participle of *qaṭaʿa*, to cut, to sever, to decide — Arabic uses *qaṭʿ* for a decision in the sense of cutting off alternatives. To *cut off* a matter is to end deliberation and settle it.

The grammatical construction — *mā kuntu* plus the active participle — denotes a continuing past habit: "I was not, and am not, the kind of person who..." The queen is not describing this occasion; she is describing her character, and she is describing it to the people who have observed it. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the statement serves both as an explanation of why she has convened them and as an assurance that their presence is not decorative: nothing has ever been decided without them, and nothing will be.

*Tashhadūn* — "until you are present, until you witness" — is from *sh-h-d*, to be present, to witness, to testify. The same root gives *shahādah*, testimony and martyrdom, and *shāhid*, witness. The queen requires the physical presence of her council at the moment of decision, not their written advice in advance. The commentators note the wisdom: a decision made in the presence of witnesses is a decision those witnesses own, and a council that has been present at the making of a policy will defend it afterward. A council consulted by memorandum will not.

**Why Consultation Is Legally and Logically Required**

The Islamic tradition's argument for consultation rests on several grounds, and the commentators state them in connection with verses 3:159 and 42:38.

The **epistemic** ground: no individual sees a complex matter from every angle. Al-Rāzī develops this at length in his commentary on 3:159 — the Prophet ﷺ, though given revelation, consulted his Companions in matters of worldly strategy because the information required was distributed among them. The Battle of Badr is the canonical instance: the Prophet ﷺ chose a campsite, and al-Ḥubāb ibn al-Mundhir asked whether the choice was revelation or opinion; on being told it was opinion, he proposed a better position, and it was adopted. The report is recorded by Ibn Isḥāq and Ibn Hishām and cited throughout the commentary literature.

The **political** ground: 3:159 states it directly — consultation keeps people gathered around the leader rather than dispersed from him. A person who has been asked is a person who has been counted, and a person who has been counted does not defect.

The **moral** ground: consultation is an act of humility, and humility is the opposite of the *ʿuluww* that verse 14 identified as the root of rejection. A ruler who consults has already declared, by the act, that he is not above being informed.

The **legal** ground: 42:38 lists consultation among the defining characteristics of the believers:

> *"And those who respond to their Lord, and establish the prayer, and whose affair is [by] consultation among them, and who spend from what We have provided them."* (Qur'an 42:38)

The verse places *shūrā* between prayer and charity — between the vertical duty to God and the horizontal duty to property — and makes it one of the marks of the community of faith. That a pagan queen in Yemen practices it, while the *malaʾ* of Noah, Pharaoh, and Makkah do not, is a deliberate contrast in the Qur'an's presentation, and the commentators do not soften it.

**The Council's Answer, and What Consultation Actually Produced**

Verse 33 will report the council's response: a statement of military capacity and an explicit deferral to the queen. Al-Rāzī considers their response a good one, balanced between deference and a display of strength. Others note that Bilqīs likely knew more than they did in any case, and hence her question was more diplomatic than practical (Ibn Kathīr records this).

Both observations are correct and both are important. The council's answer is *militarily* strong and *politically* empty — they tell her what they can do, not what she should do. If consultation is understood as the extraction of useful judgment from advisers, this consultation did not produce any. And yet it was not wasted, for three reasons the narrative makes visible.

It confirmed the queen's authority in front of the men who held the army. It established, on the record, that the coming decision was made after consultation and not in contempt of the council. And it revealed to the queen that her advisers' contribution was capacity rather than judgment — information she needed before deciding, and which she could only learn by asking.

This is a realistic and unsentimental account of consultation, and it is more useful than an idealized one. Consultation frequently fails to produce good answers. It still produces *known* positions, tested loyalties, and shared responsibility, all of which are prerequisites for a decision that will be obeyed. The queen consults, receives little, and then in verse 34 delivers an analysis of conquest far more sophisticated than anything her council offered — and they do not dispute it. Had she not consulted them, they might have.

**Application: The Habit Named in the Verse**

The verse's practical instruction is contained in its second half, where the queen describes not a single act but a settled disposition: *I was never one to decide a matter until you are present.* Consultation as an event is easy to fake and easy to abandon under pressure. Consultation as a character trait — a ruler known never to have decided alone — survives pressure, because the ruler himself relies on it.

The tradition supplies the Prophetic endorsement of this disposition in the report recorded by al-Ṭabarānī and cited by al-Ghazālī and Ibn Taymiyyah:

> *"Whoever consults has not acted rashly, and whoever seeks the good outcome (istakhāra) has not been disappointed, and whoever is sparing with what he has has not become poor."* — and the well-known statement: *"No one who consults regrets it."* (recorded in various forms; al-Ṭabarānī, al-Bazzār)

And the Prophet ﷺ himself was, by the testimony of his Companions, the most consulting of men in matters of worldly strategy. Abū Hurayrah said, recorded by al-Tirmidhī:

> *"I have not seen anyone more given to consulting his Companions than the Messenger of God ﷺ."* (al-Tirmidhī, in the Shamāʾil; and cf. 3:159)

A queen who worships the sun practices this. The verse reports it without comment, and the silence is the Qur'an's way of saying that the practice is good wherever it is found — and that the one who has it is already closer to the truth than the one who does not, whatever else either of them believes.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:33

> **They said, "We are possessed of strength and possessed of great might. But the command is thine; so consider what thou wouldst command."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Council's Answer in Three Parts**

*Qālū naḥnu ulū quwwatin wa ulū baʾsin shadīdin wa'l-amru ilayki fa-nẓurī mādhā taʾmurīn* — "They said: 'We are men of strength and men of severe might; but the affair is yours, so consider what you would command.'"

The response has a structure that al-Rāzī analyzed with care, and which the source commentary notes he considered a good one: it is balanced between a display of strength and a deference to the queen. The council begins with what it has, ends with what she holds, and places the decision between them.

**Ulū Quwwah: Men of Strength**

*Ulū* is the plural possessive construction, "those possessing, men of" — the same form as *ulū'l-albāb*, "those possessed of intellect," and *ulū'l-ʿilm*, "those possessed of knowledge," both of which the Qur'an uses for the people who understand its signs. *Quwwah* is from *q-w-y*, strength, power, capacity — the same root as *qawī*, strong, one of the Divine Names (*al-Qawiyy*, 8:52, 22:74), and *quwwah* is used in the Qur'an of military preparation:

> *"And prepare against them whatever you are able of strength (quwwah) and of steeds of war, that you may thereby strike terror into the enemy of God and your enemy."* (Qur'an 8:60)

The council's first claim is thus a claim of capacity, stated in the Qur'an's own vocabulary for military readiness.

**Ulū Baʾsin Shadīd: Men of Severe Might**

*Baʾs* is from *b-ʾ-s*, and it carries two related meanings in Arabic: severity and misfortune. *Baʾs* is hardship and affliction (*al-baʾsāʾ*, used in the Qur'an of the suffering that befalls peoples, 2:214, 6:42); and *baʾs* is also the severity of combat, the fierceness of an attack. The Qur'an uses the word of battle:

> *"And We made his kingdom strong and gave him wisdom and decisive speech."* — cf. 38:20, where *ḥikmah* and *faṣl al-khiṭāb* are given to David

> *"And when the fear departed, they assailed you with sharp tongues"* — and: *"God is severe in punishment (shadīdu'l-ʿiqāb)"* (2:196, 2:211)

*Shadīd* is from *sh-d-d*, tightness, firmness, intensity — the same root as *shiddah*, severity, and *tashdīd*, emphasis. *Baʾsin shadīd* is therefore "intense fierceness," "severe combat capability."

The two claims together — *quwwah* and *baʾsin shadīd* — cover the two components of military capacity as the ancient world understood them: the strength of numbers and equipment, and the ferocity of the men. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the notables' words pertain to their worldly power and military strength, the implication being that they were capable of supporting her with their strength in battle.

**Wa'l-Amru Ilayki: The Deference**

*"But the affair is yours."* The particle *ilā* with the pronoun gives *ilayki* — "to you, resting with you." The council states, without qualification, that the decision belongs to the queen.

This clause has been much discussed because it follows immediately upon a display of strength, and the juxtaposition is significant. Al-Rāzī's assessment — that the response is a good one, balanced between deference and a demonstration of capability — identifies the political wisdom precisely. A council that answers a ruler's request for guidance with pure deference ("whatever you decide") is useless: it contributes nothing and accepts nothing. A council that answers with pure capability ("we can fight") is dangerous: it invites the ruler to use the capability and thereby shifts the decision toward the soldiers. The correct answer names both — here is what we can do, and here is who decides — and verse 33 does exactly that in eleven words.

Ibn Kathīr records the further observation that Bilqīs likely knew more than they did in any case, and hence her question was more diplomatic than practical. On this reading the consultation was not primarily an extraction of information; it was a public establishment of the decision's legitimacy. The council's answer confirms the reading: they had no analysis to offer, only capacity to pledge and deference to express. And the queen, having obtained the pledge and the deference, proceeds in verse 34 to supply the analysis herself.

**Fa-Nẓurī Mādhā Taʾmurīn: "Consider What You Would Command"**

The final clause uses the verb *nẓar* — the same verb of verses 27 and 28, where Solomon says *"we shall see"* and *"observe what they return."* The word appears three times in eight verses, marking the sūrah's interest in *deliberate looking*: Solomon examining the bird's claim, Solomon observing the recipients' response, and the council asking the queen to examine her own command.

*Taʾmurīn* is from *ʾ-m-r*, to command — the same root as *amr*, affair and command, which appears twice in the verse (*wa'l-amru ilayki... mādhā taʾmurīn*). The council's phrase is thus self-referential in a precise way: the affair (*amr*) is yours, so consider what you will command (*taʾmurīn*). They have given her the authority and named the act by which she will exercise it.

**The Verse as a Portrait of a Functioning Hierarchy**

The exchange between verses 32 and 33 is the Qur'an's most detailed account of a deliberative process, and its realism deserves notice. Nothing in it is idealized. The council does not offer wisdom; it offers muscle and loyalty. The queen does not receive a solution; she receives a mandate. The consultation does not end the deliberation — it ends the *authorization* of the deliberation, and the queen goes on to think.

This is what actual governance looks like, and the Qur'an's willingness to depict it accurately is part of its claim on the reader's trust. A text that could describe a council of war so precisely could also describe a valley of ants, a fire in a valley, and a throne transported across a continent — and the reader who has found the political detail accurate has a reason to consider the rest.

**The Contrast With the Other Councils of the Qur'an**

Verse 33 gains its full significance against the Qur'an's other ruling councils, and the contrast is systematic.

Pharaoh's council responds to Moses with a plan to kill him and to preserve the existing order:

> *"And Pharaoh said: 'Let me kill Moses, and let him call upon his Lord. Verily I fear that he will change your religion, or that he will cause corruption to appear in the land.'"* (Qur'an 40:26)

> *"And the notables among Pharaoh's people said: 'Verily this is a skilled sorcerer who would drive you out of your land.' So what do you command?"* (Qur'an 7:109–110, 26:34–35)

Note the identical formula — *fa-mādhā taʾmurūn*, "so what do you command?" — used in 26:35 by Pharaoh's notables and in 27:33, in the feminine, by Bilqīs's notables to her. The Qur'an has deliberately rhymed the two councils. Pharaoh's council asks him what he commands in order to execute a prophet; Bilqīs's council tells her the command is hers in order to receive a decision. The same words, the same setting, opposite outcomes — and the difference is entirely in the character of the one holding the *amr*.

Thamūd's council is worse. This sūrah will describe nine of them at verse 48 as men who *"worked corruption in the land and did not set matters aright,"* and at verse 49 as conspirators who swore by God to murder their prophet by night and then to lie about it. There is no consultation there at all — only plotting.

And the *malaʾ* of Makkah, addressed throughout the Makkan revelation, respond to the Prophet ﷺ with the accusation recorded at 38:6: *"And the notables among them departed: 'Walk away and be steadfast toward your gods — verily this is a thing desired.'"* Their deliberation produces not a decision but a conspiracy to hold the line.

Against all three, verse 33 presents a council that states its capacity, defers its authority, and awaits a lawful command. The Qur'an's political imagination is not anti-elite; it is anti-*ʿuluww*. Where the notables submit the affair to a decision-maker and accept her judgment, they function. Where they exalt themselves, they plot. The distinction is the same one Solomon's letter drew in verse 31, and the council of Sheba — a pagan council, addressed by a pagan queen — passes the test that Pharaoh's council failed.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:34

> **She said, "Verily, kings, when they enter a town, corrupt it, and make the most honorable of its people the most abased. They will do likewise."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Political Theory in One Sentence**

*Qālat inna'l-mulūka idhā dakhalū qaryatan afsadūhā wa jaʿalū aʿizzata ahlihā adhillatan wa kadhalika yafʿalūn* — "She said: 'Verily kings, when they enter a town, corrupt it and make the honored of its people abased — and thus do they act.'"

The queen has heard her council's pledge of strength. Her response is not to accept it or reject it but to explain, in a single generalization, what war would actually produce. And the generalization she offers is not about her own city. It is about *kings* — the class to which she belongs and to which her correspondent belongs.
