She states the behavior of conquering sovereigns as a law, and she includes herself in the category by using it.

Al-Rāzī records that this sentence is the reason the tradition judged her worthy to rule: it is the observation of a person who understands power from inside, not of a person who fears it from outside. Ibn ʿArabī, in the *Fuṣūṣ al-ḥikam*, notes that her handling of the whole episode demonstrated her superiority and worthiness to govern.

**Inna'l-Mulūk: The Plural of the Category**

*Al-mulūk* is the plural of *malik*, king — from *m-l-k*, to own, to possess, to hold sovereignty. The same root gives *malakūt*, dominion, *mulk*, kingship, and *mālik*, owner; and it is the root of the Divine Name *al-Malik*, the Sovereign, and of *Mālik yawmi'l-dīn*, "Master of the Day of Judgment" (1:4).

The queen's use of the plural is essential to her argument. She is not accusing Solomon. She is describing a class and its regular behavior, and leaving Solomon's membership in it to be determined by evidence. This is a methodologically sophisticated move, and the Qur'an preserves it without comment — which is to say, approvingly.

The verse's structure is that of an empirical generalization: *idhā dakhalū* — "when they enter" — a conditional introduced by *idhā*, which in Arabic denotes a condition expected to occur, not a hypothetical. Kings do enter towns. When they do, the following happens. The conclusion *wa kadhalika yafʿalūn* — "and thus do they act" — closes the generalization with a present-tense habitual verb, confirming that the pattern is observed, repeated, and reliable.

**Afsadūhā: The Verb of Corruption**

The verb *afsadūhā* is Form IV from *f-s-d*, to spoil, corrupt, ruin, put out of order — the same root as *fasād*, corruption, and the opposite of *ṣalāḥ*, soundness, and *iṣlāḥ*, setting aright. This is the Qur'an's central vocabulary for social destruction, and it appears at the two poles of the sūrah:

At verse 14, Pharaoh's people are named *al-mufsidīn* — "the workers of corruption" — and the verse ends with *"behold how the workers of corruption fared in the end."*

At verse 48, Thamūd's nine conspirators are described as *"working corruption in the land and not setting matters aright"* — *yufsidūna fī'l-arḍ wa lā yuṣliḥūn*.

And the Qur'an legislates on the subject:

> *"And do not work corruption in the earth after its rectification, and call upon Him in fear and hope. Verily the mercy of God is near unto the doers of good."* (Qur'an 7:56)

> *"And when he turns away, he strives in the land to work corruption therein, and to destroy the tillage and the stock; and God loves not corruption."* (Qur'an 2:205)

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'Do not work corruption in the earth,' they say: 'We are only rectifiers.' Verily it is they who are the workers of corruption, but they are not aware."* (Qur'an 2:11–12)

The last of these is especially relevant. The corrupters believe themselves to be rectifiers, and they are *not aware* — the same phrase, in a different form, that the ant used of Solomon's hosts at verse 18: *"while they are unaware."* The sūrah has planted the vocabulary and is now harvesting it: corruption is done by people who do not perceive that they are doing it, whether they are marching armies or plotting aristocrats.

Bilqīs's insight is precisely that the *kings themselves* may not intend the corruption. Conquest is undertaken for reasons of state, security, tribute, or glory; what it produces is *fasād*, the ruin of the town's order, and the ruin is not usually the aim. The queen is describing an unintended consequence that follows with the regularity of an intended one — which is exactly what makes it a law rather than an accident.

**"And Make the Honored of Its People Abased": The Mechanism of Conquest**

*Wa jaʿalū aʿizzata ahlihā adhillatan* — "and they make the honored ones of its people abased." Two active participles in opposition: *aʿizzah* (plural of *ʿazīz*, honored, mighty, dear, unassailable — the same root as the Divine Name *al-ʿAzīz* discussed at verse 9) and *adhillah* (plural of *dhālīl*, abased, humiliated, made low — from *dh-l-l*, lowliness, subjection, the root of *dhull*, humiliation, and of *dhallala*, to make tractable, as in *"and subjected for you the ships"*, 14:32).

The observation is a statement of political sociology and it is accurate to the mechanics of empire in every age. A conquering power does not merely take a town's wealth; it dismantles the town's hierarchy. The people who were *aʿizzah* — the local nobles, the judges, the priests, the merchants, the landholders, the teachers, the ones whose standing gave the town its internal order — are systematically reduced, because their standing is the only rival authority in the place. Some are killed, some are dispossessed, some are co-opted into a subordinate role, some are made examples of. What remains is a population without leadership, dependent for status on the conqueror's favor.

The Qur'an describes Pharaoh's Egypt in exactly these terms, and the parallel is deliberate:

> *"Verily Pharaoh exalted himself in the land and made its people into factions, oppressing a party among them, slaughtering their sons and sparing their women. Verily he was among the workers of corruption."* (Qur'an 28:4)

Pharaoh *exalted himself* (the *ʿuluww* of verse 14), *made its people into factions* (dividing the hierarchy so that no group could cohere), and *oppressed a party among them* (the reduction of the *aʿizzah*). Bilqīs has described the same operation from the perspective of the town that receives it. The two verses — 28:4 and 27:34 — are the same phenomenon seen from the throne and from the street.

**Wa Kadhalika Yafʿalūn: The Closing Generalization**

*"And thus do they act"* — or, as some read it, "and thus will they act," referring specifically to Solomon and his hosts. Al-Ṭabarī records that the queen uttered this sentence in response to her council's suggestion that they were strong enough to resist, meaning that if Solomon were to enter their land, destruction would ensue. Al-Qurṭubī notes the same: the sentence is her answer to the offer of *quwwah* and *baʾs shadīd* in verse 33.

The logical structure of her rebuttal is worth setting out, because it is a genuinely good argument. Her council said: we can fight. Her response was: fighting means they enter. They enter means the town is corrupted and its honored people abased. Therefore the offer of strength is not an answer to the problem; it is a route to the problem. Strength on our side does not prevent the corruption — it guarantees the invasion that produces it.

This is reasoning of a high order, and it is why the verse has been cited in the Islamic tradition as a text on the real costs of war and on the responsibility of rulers to calculate them. The council's contribution (v. 33) was a statement of capacity. The queen's contribution (v. 34) is a statement of consequence. Governance requires the second, and the second is rarer.

**The Sufi Reading: Kings as Inspirations**

The source commentary records an allegorical reading from Ibn ʿAṭāʾillāh al-Iskandarī in his *Kitāb al-Ḥikam*, in which the kings symbolize the inspirations that assail the heart from the spiritual world. When these inspirations are real and true, they enter the town of the heart, topple the dominance of its worldly desires, make those desires the most abased, and replace them with spiritual aspirations no longer subordinate to the appetites of the soul.

The allegory is coherent with the plain sense rather than a substitute for it, and it rests on a real feature of the verse: the operation described is a *reversal of hierarchy*. What was honored becomes abased; what was abased becomes honored. The Qur'an uses exactly this structure of the spiritual life elsewhere:

> *"And as for him who feared the station of his Lord and forbade the soul its caprice, verily the Garden shall be his refuge."* (Qur'an 79:40–41)

> *"Verily the soul is a commander of evil, save that upon which my Lord has mercy."* (Qur'an 12:53)

The soul that commands evil is the *ʿazīz* in the town of the heart; the inspirations of truth are the entering kings who abase it. And the reversal is not destruction but reordering — which is the same distinction Bilqīs's sentence implies. The town is *corrupted* only when the entering force is corrupt; when it is true, the entering force reorders the town rightly. Ibn ʿAṭāʾillāh specifies that his reading applies to inspirations that are *real and true*, and the qualification preserves the verse's plain warning about kings who are neither.

**Why a Queen Says This About Kings**

The final observation on verse 34 is about the speaker. Bilqīs is a monarch. She possesses *quwwah* and *baʾs shadīd* of her own; her council has just told her so. She has a throne that the hoopoe called mighty. And she says, in her first substantive contribution to the deliberation, that kings are a class whose entry into a town produces corruption and abasement.

This is the sentence of a person who does not identify with her office. She can describe the behavior of her own category from outside it, which is the capacity most rulers lack and which the Qur'an elsewhere identifies as the mark of the just:

> *"O you who believe! Be upright for God, witnesses in equity, and let not hatred of a people incite you not to act equitably. Act equitably — that is nearer unto reverence."* (Qur'an 5:8)

> *"O David, verily We have made thee a viceroy in the earth; so judge between people with truth, and follow not caprice, lest it lead thee astray from the way of God."* (Qur'an 38:26)

The verse's practical application extends to every person who holds any power at all — a manager, a parent, a landlord, a senior colleague, a committee chair. The test Bilqīs passes is the capacity to say, about one's own class and one's own conduct, what one would say about someone else's. A person who can describe the predictable damage his own power does, in the third person, has already taken the first step toward not doing it. The queen takes that step in verse 34, and in verse 35 she acts on it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:35

> **I will send a gift to them, and observe what the envoys bring back."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Decision, and Its Character as a Test**

*Wa innī mursilatun ilayhim bi-hadiyyatin fa-nāẓiratun bimā yarjiʿu'l-mursalūn* — "And verily I am sending unto them a gift, and I shall see with what the envoys return."

Having rejected war as a policy (v. 34), Bilqīs proposes an experiment. The verse is short and its content is often passed over, but the commentators treat it as the pivot of the narrative: it is the decision that converts a diplomatic crisis into a process of discovery, and it is the reason the queen ends up in Solomon's court rather than at war with him.

**Hadiyyah: The Word and Its Distinction**

*Hadiyyah* is from *h-d-y*, the same root as *hudā*, guidance — the gift is so called because it *leads* the giver toward the recipient and *leads* goodwill between them. Arabic distinguishes *hadiyyah* from *ṣadaqah* (alms given to the needy) and from *hibah* (a grant without occasion): the *hadiyyah* is a gift offered to an equal or a superior, on an occasion, in order to establish or confirm a relation.

The Prophet ﷺ practiced and encouraged it, and the ḥadīth literature is full of the practice:

> *"Give gifts to one another and you will love one another."* (al-Bukhārī in al-Adab al-Mufrad 594; graded ḥasan by al-Albānī)

> *"The Prophet ﷺ used to accept a gift and used to give a recompense for it."* (al-Bukhārī 2585)

> *"O women believers, let not a neighbor woman despise [to give] her neighbor a gift, even if it is only a sheep's trotter."* (al-Bukhārī 2566; Muslim 1030)

Bilqīs's gift is of a different order in scale — the commentators and storytellers enumerate treasures at length — but the function is the same: to establish a relation before any relation exists, and to test the character of the recipient.

**Why a Gift Rather Than a Reply**

The commentators explain the queen's reasoning, and al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī give it in terms that show her political acumen. Solomon's letter demanded submission. A direct refusal would precipitate the invasion she has just argued against; a direct acceptance would surrender her kingdom without knowing what she was surrendering to. A gift does neither. It is a response that is not an answer — a gesture that opens a channel without committing to a position.

More than that, it is a diagnostic. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both note that the gift was designed to discover what kind of man Solomon was. A king who accepts a gift has revealed that he can be bought, that his demand for submission was a negotiating position, and that his kingdom can be managed with money. A king who refuses a gift has revealed that he cannot be bought, that his demand is principled, and that the matter at issue is not political but religious. Bilqīs needs to know which she is dealing with before she can decide anything, and the gift is the cheapest and most reliable instrument available for finding out.

This is a method of considerable generality, and the Qur'an's presentation of it without criticism indicates approval. When a person needs to know what someone else actually values, the most efficient test is not to ask him but to offer him something and watch. A man's response to an offer he did not request discloses his priorities more accurately than any statement of them, because the response is unscripted.

**Fa-Nāẓiratun Bimā Yarjiʿu'l-Mursalūn: The Same Verb of Deliberate Looking**

*"And I shall see with what the envoys return."* The verb *nāẓirah* is the feminine active participle of *n-ẓar* — to look, to consider, to await — the same root Solomon used twice in verses 27 and 28 (*sananẓur*, *fa-nẓur*) and that the council used in verse 33 (*fa-nẓurī*). The word appears four times in nine verses, and it is the sūrah's governing verb for the right posture toward a claim: *look, wait, examine.*

The symmetry between Solomon and Bilqīs at this point is exact and the commentators have noted it. Solomon sent a letter and said "observe what they return" (v. 28). Bilqīs sends a gift and says "I shall see what the envoys return" (v. 35). Both have dispatched something, withdrawn, and committed to learning from the response. Both have used the identical verb. The narrative is showing two minds operating by the same method on opposite sides of a continent — and the method is the reason the encounter ends in faith rather than in war.

*Bimā yarjiʿu'l-mursalūn* — "with what the envoys return" — uses *r-j-ʿ*, the root of return discussed at verse 28. The envoys will *return* with an answer, and the answer will determine everything. The Qur'an's eschatological use of the same root — *unto God is your return* — is not distant from this: what returns determines what follows, in diplomacy and in judgment alike.

**On the Details of the Gift, and Why They Are Not in the Qur'an**

The source commentary records that the commentators give details of the story, describing sometimes at length what the gifts were — gold, jewels, garments, a crown, and in some accounts a thousand male servants dressed as women and a thousand female servants dressed as men, offered as a test of Solomon's prophetic knowledge. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī report these traditions; al-Rāzī dismisses such details as irrelevant to the story.

The scholarly position here is worth stating, because it is a model of the tradition's own critical method. The details come almost entirely from *isrāʾīliyyāt* — reports transmitted from Jewish and Christian sources by converts to Islam, which the Prophet ﷺ neither confirmed nor denied and which the Muslim tradition therefore treats under a specific rule. Al-Bukhārī records the guidance:

> *"Convey from me even a verse, and relate from the Children of Israel without restriction; and whoever lies upon me deliberately, let him take his seat in the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 3461)

And Ibn ʿAbbās's reported rebuke, recorded by al-Bukhārī:

> *"O Muslims, how do you ask the People of the Book about anything, when your Book that God sent down upon His Prophet is the freshest news of God, unmixed, and you recite it? God has told you that the People of the Book altered the Book and changed it, and wrote with their own hands what was not from God and said: 'It is from God' — to sell it for a small price."* (al-Bukhārī 7363)

The rule the tradition drew from these reports is threefold: what agrees with the Qur'an and Sunnah is accepted; what contradicts them is rejected; and what neither confirms nor contradicts is neither believed nor disbelieved. Ibn Kathīr applies this rule explicitly at verse 35 and again at verse 44, seeing little value in the folkloric material and noting that it originated in Arab legends and in the oral traditions of converts from Judaism and Christianity.

The Qur'an's own silence about the gift's contents is therefore meaningful. It tells us that a gift was sent, that it was refused, and why it was refused — and it does not tell us what was in the boxes, because the boxes are not the point. The point is the test, and the result.

**The Wisdom of a Decision That Delays a Decision**

Verse 35 presents a form of judgment that modern readers often find difficult to classify: a decision *not to decide yet*, made deliberately and with a plan. Bilqīs has not committed to war or to submission. She has committed to obtaining information, at low cost, with a reversible action, within a bounded time.

This is the structure of good judgment under uncertainty, and the Qur'an presents it approvingly in a pagan queen. The alternative courses were all worse: refuse and be invaded, submit and be absorbed without understanding what she had submitted to, or do nothing and let Solomon's next move determine hers. The gift is the only option that is simultaneously active, low-cost, reversible, and informative.

The practical application is immediate and general. When confronted with a demand one is not ready to answer — from a person, an employer, an institution, or one's own conscience — the correct move is rarely either compliance or defiance. It is usually a probe: an action that costs little, that reveals the other party's character, that keeps one's own options open, and that produces information. Bilqīs sent a gift. The reader can send a question, request a document, propose a small joint step, or offer a modest gesture and watch what comes back. What comes back will be worth more than anything the other party has said.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:36

> **But when it came to Solomon, he said, "Do you offer me wealth? What God has given me is better than what He has given you. Nay, but it is you who exult in your gift!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Test Answered in the Negative**

*Falammā jāʾa Sulaymāna qāla a-tumiddūnani bi-mālin fa-mā ātāniya Llāhu khayrun mimmā ātākum bal antum bi-hadiyyatikum tafraḥūn* — "Then when it came unto Solomon, he said: 'Do you extend to me wealth? What God has given me is better than what He has given you. Nay, but you are the ones who rejoice in your gift!'"

The verse reports the gift's arrival and Solomon's response in the same breath, and the response is immediate. There is no deliberation, no consultation with his council, no examination of the treasure. The refusal is instantaneous, which tells the reader that the question it answers was already settled in Solomon's mind long before the caravan arrived.

**A-Tumiddūnani Bi-Mālin: The Verb of Extension**

*A-tumiddūnani* is the interrogative of Form IV from *m-d-d*, to extend, to stretch out, to supply, to prolong — the same root as *madda*, to stretch, *imdād*, reinforcement or supply, and *mudd*, a measure. The Qur'an uses the verb of God's supplying of provision and of Satan's extension of temptation:

> *"And We have made the wealth and the children of the disbelievers a trial for them"* — cf. the usage at 9:55, 23:55

> *"And those who deny Our signs — We shall extend unto them gradually (sanastadrijuhum) from whence they know not."* (Qur'an 7:182)

> *"And let not their wealth and their children impress thee. God wills only to punish them therewith in this world, and that their souls depart while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 9:85)

The double *nūn* in *tumiddūnani* marks both the second-person plural subject and the first-person singular object — "do you (plural) extend to me (singular)?" The grammar preserves the confrontation exactly: a nation's treasury being offered to one man.

Solomon's opening word is a question, and the question is itself the refusal. He does not say "I refuse your gift." He says "are you supplying me with wealth?" — a formulation that treats the offer as incomprehensible rather than as insulting. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the interrogative expresses astonishment: how could you imagine that this is what I lack?

**Fa-Mā Ātāniya Llāhu Khayrun Mimmā Ātākum: The Comparison**

*"What God has given me is better than what He has given you."* The verb *ātā* — Form IV from *ʾ-t-y*, to give, to grant — appears twice, and both times with God as the subject: *ātāniya Llāh*, "God has given me," and *ātākum*, "He has given you." This is the most important grammatical feature of the verse, and al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both draw out its significance.

Solomon does not say "what I have is better than what you have." He says "what *God has given me* is better than what *God has given you*." Both possessions are located in the same source. The comparison is not between two owners but between two gifts, and the framing does three things at once.

It **refuses the transaction**. A gift can only be a transaction between parties who understand themselves to be owners. Solomon's phrasing dissolves the ownership on both sides, and with it the possibility of exchange: I cannot take your property, because it is not your property; and you cannot buy mine, because it is not for sale.

It **removes the insult**. To say "my wealth exceeds yours" is a boast; to say "what God gave me exceeds what God gave you" is a statement of fact about a distribution neither party arranged. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the Quraysh's wealth was no match for Solomon's in any case, and that Solomon was a prophet and therefore not motivated by worldly acquisition for its own sake. But the deeper reason for the phrasing is theological rather than comparative: Solomon has correctly identified the giver, and a person who has identified the giver cannot be bribed by the gift.

It **echoes verse 16**. Solomon's own announcement was *"we have been given (ūtīnā) of all things"* — the same verb, the same passive, the same understanding that what he holds was given. The hoopoe's report of Bilqīs used the identical construction: *"she has been given (ūtiyat) of all things"* (v. 23). Both were given. One of them knows it. The verse is the moment at which the sūrah shows what that knowledge does when it is tested with an actual offer.

**Khayr: The Word for What Is Better**

*Khayr* is from the root *kh-y-r*, goodness, excellence, preference — the same root as *akhyar*, better, *khiyarah*, the right of choice, and *istikharah*, seeking the good in a decision. The Qur'an uses *khayr* both as a noun ("good, wealth, benefit") and as a comparative ("better").

The comparative structure *khayrun min* — "better than" — is one of the Qur'an's most frequent argumentative forms, and it is used to relativize worldly goods against divine ones:

> *"The adornment of this world's life, and to God is the best return (al-maʾāb al-ḥasan)."* — cf. 3:14, which lists the adorned things and then says: *"Say: 'Shall I inform you of something better than that? For those who revere [God] there are Gardens with their Lord, beneath which rivers flow, wherein they abide...'"* (Qur'an 3:15)

> *"Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world; but the abiding good deeds (al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt) are better with thy Lord for reward, and better for hope."* (Qur'an 18:46)

> *"Say: 'The enjoyment of this world is little, and the Hereafter is better for him who reveres [God].'"* (Qur'an 4:77)

> *"And whatsoever you have been given is the enjoyment of the life of this world and its adornment; but what is with God is better and more lasting. Do you not then use your intellect?"* (Qur'an 28:60)

The last of these is from al-Qaṣaṣ, the sūrah immediately following al-Naml, and it makes the same comparison in the same vocabulary, ending with a question about the intellect. Solomon's sentence in verse 36 is that argument applied to an actual pile of gold: what God has given me — prophethood, knowledge, the language of birds, a kingdom no one after me will hold — is *khayr* from what God has given you — treasure, gardens, a throne.

**Bal Antum Bi-Hadiyyatikum Tafraḥūn: The Diagnosis**

*"Nay, but you are the ones who rejoice in your gift."* The particle *bal* is one of Arabic's most powerful small words: it strikes out what preceded and substitutes something else — "no, rather, on the contrary." The sentence that follows *bal* is the corrected diagnosis.

The verb *tafraḥūn* is from *f-r-ḥ*, joy, delight, exultation — the same root as *faraḥ*, happiness, and *farḥān*, delighted. The Qur'an uses the word both positively and negatively, and the negative use is precisely the one here:

> *"That is because, when their messengers came unto them with the clear signs, they rejoiced (fariḥū) in the knowledge they possessed"* — i.e., they were smug in it (Qur'an 40:83)

> *"When evil touches them they are cast down, and when good touches them they are niggardly"* — and: *"Rejoice not that God will enrich them"* — cf. 9:81, 28:76

> *"Until, when they rejoiced (fariḥū) in what they were given, We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair."* (Qur'an 6:44)

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is exact: to exult in one's gift means the gift was a means of celebrating one's own wealth and power rather than a manifestation of generosity or goodness. The *faraḥ* Solomon identifies is the joy of display — the pleasure taken not in giving but in being seen to have enough to give.

This is why the diagnosis is prefixed with *bal antum* — "nay, *you* are." The emphatic construction places the rejoicing entirely on their side. Solomon does not rejoice in the gift; he is unmoved by it. They rejoice in it, and their rejoicing reveals what the gift was actually for: not to establish goodwill but to display capacity. A gift given to display the giver's wealth is not a gift; it is an advertisement, and Solomon names it as such.

The verse thereby completes the queen's test, though not in the way her council would have expected. Solomon has refused the gift — the diagnostic result Bilqīs sought. But he has also refused the *premise* of the gift, which is that wealth is a measure of standing. That is information Bilqīs did not ask for and will need.

**The Prophet Who Cannot Be Bought**

Al-Qurṭubī's observation that Solomon, as a prophet, was not motivated by worldly acquisition for its own sake is the theological ground of the verse, and the Qur'an states the principle for its prophets explicitly and repeatedly. Every prophet in the Qur'an says some version of the same sentence:

> *"O my people, I ask of you no reward for it; my reward falls only upon Him Who originated me. Do you not then use your intellect?"* (Qur'an 11:51 — Hūd; cf. 11:29 Noah, 26:109, 127, 145, 164, 180)

> *"Say: 'I ask of you no reward for it, save love for [my] near kin.'"* (Qur'an 42:23)

> *"Say: 'Whatever reward I might have asked of you is yours. My reward falls only upon God, and He is Witness over all things.'"* (Qur'an 34:47)

> *"And warn thy nearest kin."* — and: *"Say: 'Verily my prayer, my rite, my life and my death are for God, Lord of the worlds, with no partner unto Him. So am I commanded, and I am the first of those who submit.'"* (Qur'an 6:162–164)

The financial independence of the prophet is not a personal austerity but a structural necessity. A messenger who can be paid can be managed; a messenger who takes nothing from anyone can be refuted, opposed, imprisoned, and killed, but he cannot be purchased — and the Qur'an's argument for its messengers' credibility rests substantially on this. Verse 36 is the demonstration in narrative form: the wealthiest king in history refuses a queen's treasure, and refuses it not because it is insufficient but because it is the wrong currency.

**The Verse's Application to Every Offer a Believer Receives**

The practical reach of verse 36 is broad and immediate. Every person is offered things — money, position, approval, compromise, silence in exchange for comfort — and the offers are usually framed in the language of benefit: *a-tumiddūnani bi-māl*, "are you supplying me with wealth?" The word *imdad*, supply and reinforcement, is exactly how such offers present themselves: as support, as help, as an addition to one's resources.

The verse supplies the answer in two moves. The first is the comparison: what God has given you — faith, a mind capable of knowing Him, a conscience, the prayer, the promise of the Hereafter — is *khayr* than anything being offered. The second is the diagnosis: examine why the offer is being made, and notice whether the offerer's pleasure lies in the giving or in the display. An offer made to display the giver's power is not an offer of a thing; it is an offer of a relation, and the relation is subordination.

Solomon's refusal was possible because he had already said, in verse 16, what everything he had was. A person who has not made that statement about his own possessions cannot make Solomon's answer to an offer, because he will not know what he is being asked to trade. The sequence of the sūrah is therefore instructive: gratitude declared (v. 16), threat issued and suspended (v. 21), letter sent (v. 28), council consulted (v. 32–34), gift sent (v. 35), gift refused (v. 36). The refusal at the end is produced by the gratitude at the beginning, and it could not have been produced by anything else.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:37

> **"Return unto them, for we shall come upon them with hosts they cannot withstand, and we shall expel them hence, abased, and they shall be humbled."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to the Envoys**

*Irjiʿ ilayhim fa-la-naʾtiyannahum bi-junūdin lā qibala lahum bihā wa-la-nukhrijannahum minhā adhillatan wa hum ṣāghirūn* — "Return unto them! For we shall come upon them with hosts they have no power to resist, and we shall expel them from it, abased, and they shall be humiliated."

Al-Ṭabarī notes that these words were spoken by Solomon to the envoy from Sheba — the same envoys Bilqīs said she would watch for in verse 35. The verse is thus the return cargo of her test, and it is the answer that will determine what she does next.

**Irjiʿ Ilayhim: The Imperative of Dismissal**

*Irjiʿ* is the imperative of *rajaʿa*, to return — the same root as *yarjiʿūn* in verse 28 and *yarjiʿu'l-mursalūn* in verse 35. The verb that both parties used for the envoys' return is now used by Solomon as a command: go back. The dismissal is complete and contains no counter-offer, no negotiation, no conditions for acceptance.

The commentators note what the dismissal does and does not say. It does not accept the gift. It does not declare war immediately. It does not open a dialogue. It states what will happen if the demand of verse 31 is not met — and it leaves the interval between the envoy's return and the arrival of the hosts for the recipients to decide in.

**Fa-La-Naʾtiyannahum: The Stacked Emphases**

The clause *fa-la-naʾtiyannahum* — "for we shall certainly come upon them" — carries two emphatic particles: the *lām* of oath and the heavy *nūn* (*nūn al-tawkīd al-thaqīlah*). Arabic stacks these when a speaker intends to remove all possibility of doubt. The same construction appears in the second clause: *wa-la-nukhrijannahum* — "and we shall certainly expel them."

The effect is a formal, sworn declaration of intent, and the commentators note that this is the language of a sovereign making an unconditional statement about the future. The two emphatic verbs are followed by two descriptors of the result — *adhillatan* (abased) and *ṣāghirūn* (humiliated) — and the doubling of the description matches the doubling of the oath.

**Bi-Junūdin Lā Qibala Lahum Bihā: Hosts Beyond Their Capacity**

*Junūd* is the plural of *jund*, an army, a host, a gathered force — the word used at verse 17 of Solomon's muster (*junūduhu mina'l-jinni wa'l-insi wa'l-ṭayr*). The Qur'an uses *junūd* of God's own forces:

> *"And unto God belong the hosts of the heavens and the earth, and God is Knowing, Wise."* (Qur'an 48:4, 7)

> *"And you know not the hosts of thy Lord."* — cf. 74:31

*Qibal* is from *q-b-l*, facing, capacity, ability to confront — the construction *lā qibala lahum bihā* means "they have no facing for it," "no capacity to meet it," "no ability to stand against it." Arabic uses the phrase for a force beyond resistance, and the Qur'an uses it elsewhere of what human beings cannot oppose:

> *"And they said: 'No strength have we today against Goliath and his hosts'"* — cf. the parallel at 2:249, where the believers answer: *"How many a small host has overcome a large host by God's leave"*

The declaration is therefore not a threat of a larger army but of an *unmatchable* one — and the readers of verse 17 know exactly what is being referred to. An army containing jinn is not an army any human force can face, because jinn are not within the ordinary range of human military capacity at all. The commentators note that this is the point: Bilqīs's council offered *quwwah* and *baʾs shadīd* (v. 33), and Solomon's answer identifies a force against which neither avails.

**Wa-La-Nukhrijannahum Minhā Adhillatan: Expulsion in Humiliation**

*"And we shall certainly expel them from it, abased."* The verb *nukhriju* is from *kh-r-j*, to go out, to bring out — the same root as *yukhriju'l-khabʾa* at verse 25, "Who brings forth what is hidden." The expulsion is described with two qualifiers.

*Adhillatan* — the accusative of *dhālīl*, abased, made low — is the same root as *adhillah* in the queen's own sentence at verse 34: *"and make the honored of its people abased."* Solomon's threat uses the queen's own vocabulary. The verse thereby confirms her political theory: this is what kings do when they enter a town. Bilqīs predicted the mechanism in verse 34; Solomon announces that he possesses it in verse 37.

The commentators have noted the significance of this correspondence with care. The queen's analysis was not a slander against kings; it was an accurate description of conquest, and Solomon's own declaration demonstrates its accuracy. Which raises the question the narrative is about to answer: if Solomon is a king of the kind she described, why does she end up submitting voluntarily, and why does her city survive?

*Wa hum ṣāghirūn* — "and they shall be humiliated, made small." *Ṣāghir* is from *ṣ-gh-r*, smallness, inferiority, humiliation — the same root as *ṣaghār*, abject humiliation, and *aṣghar*, smaller, and the Qur'an uses it of the state of the disbelievers before God:

> *"And unto God prostrates whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth, of creatures and angels, and they are not arrogant."* — cf. 16:49–50

> *"They said: 'O our father, why dost thou not trust us in Joseph, though we are his sincere well-wishers?'"* — and the phrase *wa hum ṣāghirūn* appears in 12:90 of the brothers before Joseph.

The pairing of *adhillatan* and *ṣāghirūn* describes a total reduction: low in status and small in standing. The two words together name the exact opposite of the *ʿuluww* that verse 31 forbade and that verse 14 identified as the cause of Pharaoh's destruction.

**The Function of the Threat in the Narrative**

The threat in verse 37 is severe, and readers have asked why Solomon issues it after refusing a gift and before receiving any hostile act. The commentators give several reasons, and they converge.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that the refusal of the gift necessarily implied the enforcement of the original demand; having declined the substitute, Solomon had to restate the requirement and the consequence of not meeting it. Al-Qurṭubī adds that the threat was in fact a mercy in operation: it was the instrument that brought Bilqīs to Solomon's court, where she encountered the evidence that led to her submission, and thus it saved her city from the invasion it described. A threat believed produces compliance without war; a threat not believed produces war.

Ibn Kathīr notes the same and connects it to the prophetic practice: the messengers warn before they act, and the warning is itself part of the message. The Qur'an states the principle:

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

> *"Messengers, bearers of good news and warning, so that mankind should have no argument against God after the messengers."* (Qur'an 4:165)

Verse 37 is the *warning* half of the messenger's function operating at the level of statecraft. It leaves no ambiguity about the consequence, and it thereby makes the consequence avoidable — which is the entire purpose of a warning that is delivered in time.

**The Threat That Never Happens**

The most striking fact about verse 37 is that nothing in it occurs. No host marches. No town is entered. No honored person is abased. The narrative moves instead to a throne, a disguised seat, a glass pavilion, and a prayer. The threat is issued, believed, and rendered unnecessary by the belief.

This is the Qur'an's account of how power should work when it is held by someone who understands what it is for. Solomon possesses the force he describes; he states it plainly; and the plain statement achieves the objective without the force. Bilqīs's theory of conquest (v. 34) is refuted not by argument but by event: a king with unmatchable hosts entered her affairs and did not corrupt her town, because she came to him before he came to her.

The verse's practical application follows directly. A person, an institution, or a state that possesses real capacity and states it clearly, in advance, and without bluff, usually does not need to use it. A capacity that is hidden invites testing; a capacity that is exaggerated invites discovery; a capacity that is stated once and then not repeated produces the effect Solomon's produced. The condition is that the statement must be true — Solomon could say *"hosts they cannot withstand"* because he had hosts they could not withstand, and verse 17 had already shown them to the reader.

And there is a final layer. The hosts that eventually do come to Sabaʾ are not the hosts of verse 37. They are a letter, a bird, a throne transported in a glance, a disguised seat, and a floor of crystal that a queen mistakes for water. The sūrah's argument about power ends where it began: the thing that changed a nation's religion was not an army. It was evidence, delivered to a mind that had already learned, at verse 34, to look at consequences rather than at strength.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:38

> **He said, "O notables! Which of you will bring me her throne before they come unto me in submission?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Council, on the Other Side**

*Qāla yā ayyuha'l-malaʾu ayyukum yaʾtīnī bi-ʿarshihā qabla an yaʾtūnī muslimīn* — "He said: 'O notables! Which of you will bring me her throne before they come unto me as submitters?'"

The verse opens with the same vocative Bilqīs used in verse 29 — *yā ayyuha'l-malaʾ* — and it places the two councils in deliberate parallel. A queen in Yemen addresses her notables and asks for their opinion about a letter. A prophet-king in Palestine addresses his notables and asks which of them will perform a task. Both consult; both receive answers; the answers differ in kind, and the difference is the subject of the next three verses.

**When the Verse Was Spoken**

The commentators disagree about the chronology, and the source commentary records both positions. The majority hold that these words were said *after* Solomon received the gifts from Sheba — that is, following verses 36–37, when it was established that the queen and her people would be coming. Others hold that the words were spoken when the hoopoe first brought news of the Queen of Sheba and her throne, and that they follow upon Solomon's statement in verse 27: *"We shall see if you have spoken the truth."*

On the second reading the throne's transport is itself the verification of the hoopoe's report — Solomon tests the bird's claim by asking for the object the bird described, and if it arrives, the report is true. On the first reading it is a demonstration prepared for the queen's arrival. Al-Qurṭubī prefers the majority view, and the narrative's own sequence supports it: verses 39–40 report the offer and the arrival, and verse 41 has Solomon order the throne disguised *for her*, which requires that her coming is expected.

Both readings agree on the purpose, which al-Ṭabarī states: Solomon asked for the throne either to send her a message of reproof or so that he could later show it to her as a proof of his prophethood. The second is the one the narrative develops.

**"Before They Come Unto Me in Submission": The Deadline**

*Qabla an yaʾtūnī muslimīn* — "before they come unto me as submitters." The phrase reuses the exact word of Solomon's letter (v. 31): *muslimīn*. The letter demanded that they come submitting; the throne must arrive *before* they do.

The timing requirement has been analyzed by the commentators for its purpose, and the analysis illuminates the whole episode. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī explain that the throne had to be present before the queen's arrival so that it could be altered and then presented to her as a test — a test that would be impossible if she arrived first, since she would know whether her throne was still in her palace.

The phrase *muslimīn* is interpreted by some to mean in obedience to Solomon, and by others to mean "as Muslims," adherents of the religion of the One God (al-Ṭabarī records both). The second reading is supported by the narrative's outcome: when they arrive, the queen does in fact become a submitter in the religious sense (v. 44), and her submission is described with the same root — *wa aslamtu maʿa Sulaymāna li-Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*.

There is also a subtle point in the deadline itself. Solomon is asking for something to be done *before an event whose timing he does not control* — the arrival of a delegation from a country weeks away. The task is therefore bounded by an unknown date, which makes it urgent in a way a fixed deadline would not. The urgency matters for what follows: the ʿifrīt offers a time measured in hours, and the one with knowledge of the Book offers a time measured in a glance, and the difference between the two offers is the difference between the two kinds of power the verse is about to contrast.

**ʿArshihā: The Throne as the Object**

The object requested is her throne — the same throne the hoopoe described at verse 23 as *ʿarshun ʿaẓīm* and that verse 26 relativized against *"the Lord of the mighty Throne."* The sūrah has been preparing this object since the bird first mentioned it, and the preparation is now cashed.

Why the throne? The commentators give reasons that accumulate rather than compete.

It is the **symbol of her sovereignty** — the physical object in which her rule is embodied, the seat before which her nation kneels. To possess it is to possess, symbolically, what the letter demanded.

It is the **verification of the hoopoe's report** — an object described from a distance, now available for inspection. If it arrives as described, the bird told the truth.

It is the **instrument of a test of her intelligence** — as verses 41–42 will show, the throne is disguised precisely so that its owner must exercise judgment about it.

And it is the **object lesson in scale** — the throne is transported instantly, altered casually, and returned to its owner as a puzzle, and the whole sequence demonstrates what the hoopoe's theology at verse 26 stated: a throne is a small thing in the order of things governed by the Lord of *the* Throne.

**Ayyukum Yaʾtīnī: The Question That Produces Two Answers**

*"Which of you will bring me..."* The interrogative *ayyukum* invites volunteers, and the verse's placement at the head of a scene containing two volunteers (vv. 39, 40) makes it a structured comparison. Al-Naml is going to present two kinds of capacity in immediate succession, and it will let the reader compare them without comment.

The first answer comes from an *ʿifrīt* of the jinn: strength, speed measured in hours, and a claim of trustworthiness. The second comes from *"the one who had knowledge of the Book"*: speed measured in the return of a glance, and no claim at all about himself.

The comparison is the verse's real subject, and the sūrah resolves it in the following verses with a precision that requires no narration. Strength offers itself and describes itself. Knowledge offers a result and says nothing about the one who produces it. And when the result arrives, the man who asked for it — the most powerful king in the Qur'an — does not congratulate either volunteer. He looks at the throne and says: *"This is of the Bounty of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful"* (v. 40).

That is the answer to *ayyukum*: whichever of you does it, it is not from you. The question Solomon asked his council is answered, in the end, by a statement about God — which is the same way verse 36 answered Bilqīs's gift.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:39

> **An ʿifrīt among the jinn said, "I will bring it to thee before thou risest from thy place. Truly I have the strength for it, [and] am trustworthy."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Volunteer**

*Qāla ʿifrītun mina'l-jinni anā ātīka bihī qabla an taqūma min maqāmika wa innī ʿalayhi la-qawiyyun amīn* — "An ʿifrīt of the jinn said: 'I will bring it to you before you rise from your seat; and I am, for it, strong and trustworthy.'"

The verse introduces a new character in a single indefinite noun — *ʿifrītun*, "an ʿifrīt" — unnamed, unranked, identified only by kind. The indefiniteness is characteristic of the Qur'an's narrative economy: it does not need a name for a figure whose function is to be a type. And the type is described with unusual precision.

**ʿIfrīt: The Word and Its Meanings**

The word *ʿifrīt* occurs only here in the entire Qur'an, and the commentators worked extensively on its meaning. Al-Ṭabarī records that an *ʿifrīt* is a kind of jinn that is either a giant — in one account reported by Ibn Kathīr, as big as a mountain — or a mischievously clever jinn. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both give these readings; al-Rāzī adds that others say an *ʿifrīt* is a man who is foul and bold. The Arabic lexicons connect the word to *ʿafara*, dust and soil — the *ʿifrīt* is one who wallows in dust, who is rough, coarse, and fierce — and to *ʿafar*, the bold and cunning.

The Qur'an's own use of the cognate establishes the jinn connection:

> *"And [We subjected] unto Solomon the wind, and the jinn (al-shayāṭīn), every builder and diver."* (Qur'an 38:37)

> *"And among the jinn were those who worked before him by the leave of his Lord."* (Qur'an 34:12)

> *"And We made the jinn (al-jānn) from a smokeless flame of fire."* — cf. 55:15, 15:27

Al-Qurṭubī cites at this verse a ḥadīth in which the Prophet ﷺ overpowered an *ʿifrīt* that had come to disrupt his prayer — the report recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim and discussed at verse 17 above:

> *"An ʿifrīt of the jinn sought to attack me suddenly last night to break off my prayer, but God enabled me to overpower him, and I took hold of him, and I wanted to tie him to one of the pillars of the mosque so that you might all look at him in the morning. Then I remembered the supplication of my brother Solomon: 'My Lord, forgive me and grant me a kingdom that belongs to no one after me' — so I let him go, humiliated."* (al-Bukhārī 461; Muslim 541)

The ḥadīth, as the source commentary notes, supports the idea that an *ʿifrīt* is a kind of mischievous jinn — the bold, aggressive, cunning member of a species that is itself invisible and ordinarily uncontrollable by human beings.

**Anā Ātīka Bihī: The Offer and Its Self-Reference**

*"I will bring it to you."* The offer opens with the pronoun *anā* — "I" — placed before the verb, which in Arabic produces emphasis and exclusivity: "*I* am the one who will bring it." The construction is one of self-assertion, and the commentators note the contrast with verse 40, where the second volunteer makes no such claim.

The offer specifies a time: *qabla an taqūma min maqāmik* — "before you rise from your seat." Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī discuss what this period amounts to: Solomon sat in judgment and council from the morning until a certain hour, and the *ʿifrīt* is offering to complete a journey of some two thousand miles within that sitting. This is an extraordinary offer by any standard, and it establishes the scale of jinn capability: what would take a human caravan months, an *ʿifrīt* proposes to do before a man stands up.

The offer is also carefully limited. The *ʿifrīt* does not offer to bring the throne instantly. He offers a bounded time. Al-Rāzī observes that the limitation is characteristic of strength as such: strength operates within duration, however compressed, and its measure is how much it can accomplish in a given interval. The next verse will present something of a different order entirely.

**La-Qawiyyun Amīn: Two Claims About Himself**

*"And I am, for it, strong and trustworthy."* The clause carries the emphatic *inna* and the *lām* of confirmation — *wa innī ʿalayhi la-qawiyyun amīn* — one of the strongest self-assertions in the Qur'an. Two qualities are claimed, and both are relevant to the task.

*Qawiyy* — strong, powerful, capable — from *q-w-y*, the same root as *quwwah* in verse 33, where Bilqīs's council claimed *we are men of strength (ulū quwwah)*. The *ʿifrīt* claims the same quality in the singular and intensifies it by attaching it to the specific task: *ʿalayhi*, "for it, upon it" — I am strong *for this*.

*Amīn* — trustworthy, faithful, reliable — from *ʾ-m-n*, safety, security, trust. The same root gives *amānah*, trust and deposit, *īmān*, faith, *muʾmin*, believer, and *al-Amīn*, the Trustworthy — an epithet applied to the Prophet ﷺ before his mission and to the angel Gabriel (*al-Rūḥ al-Amīn*, 26:193).

The pairing of the two qualities is exactly right for the task, and it shows that the *ʿifrīt* understands what he is volunteering for. Transporting a royal throne requires strength — to lift and carry it — and it requires trustworthiness, because the object is a sovereign's most valuable symbol, transported without witnesses, by a being the owner cannot see, into the hands of a foreign king. A strong thief would be worse than useless. The *ʿifrīt* anticipates the objection and answers it in advance.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the significance of the claim's second half in the context of jinn's ordinary character: an *ʿifrīt* is by definition mischievous and bold, and its self-declaration of trustworthiness is therefore a claim against type — a claim that requires the authority of verse 34:12 (*"by the leave of his Lord"*) to be credible at all. The jinn work for Solomon under a divine mandate with a penalty attached; their trustworthiness is guaranteed not by their natures but by their subjection.

**The Psychology of the Volunteer**

There is a human — or jinn — psychology in this verse that the narrative exploits in the following one. The *ʿifrīt* speaks first, at length, with emphasis, about himself. He names his strength, names his reliability, and names a deadline. He is, in modern terms, pitching: presenting credentials, differentiating himself from other candidates, committing to a deliverable.

Verse 40 will present the contrast in the same scene. The second volunteer says one clause about the task and nothing about himself: *"I will bring it to you before your glance returns to you."* No strength claimed, no trustworthiness asserted, no self-reference at all. And the throne arrives — before the *ʿifrīt*'s offer could have been fulfilled.

The sūrah is making an argument about the relation between capacity and self-description, and the argument is that they are inversely proportional. The one who needs to describe his capability has less of it; the one who simply produces the result has more. This is a principle the Qur'an states elsewhere in explicit terms:

> *"Say: 'Praise belongs to God, and peace be upon His servants whom He has chosen.'"* (Qur'an 27:59 — this sūrah, verse 59)

> *"And the servants of the Compassionate are those who walk upon the earth gently, and when the ignorant address them, they say: 'Peace.'"* (Qur'an 25:63)

> *"So do not praise yourselves; He knows best who reverts [to Him]."* (Qur'an 53:32)

The last of these is the Qur'an's direct instruction on the matter, and verse 39 is its narrative illustration: the *ʿifrīt* praises himself in a sentence with two emphatic particles, and the verse that follows shows someone who does not.

**Why the Offer Is Recorded at All**

A reader may ask why the Qur'an reports the *ʿifrīt*'s offer, given that the offer is superseded within a verse. The reasons the commentators give are several, and together they justify the inclusion.

The offer **establishes the scale of the task**. Without it, the throne's arrival in a glance would be a bare marvel; with it, the reader understands that even jinn strength requires a full council sitting to accomplish the transport, and that what happens next is of a different order.

The offer **establishes the reality of the jinn's service**. The reader sees jinn volunteering, negotiating terms, and claiming qualifications — which confirms what verse 17 described and what 34:12–13 legislated. The jinn are not a metaphor in this narrative; they are workers with capacities and dispositions.

The offer **sets up the comparison between two kinds of power**, which is the theological point of the passage. Strength is real, useful, bounded, and self-announcing. Knowledge is real, more effective, essentially unbounded, and silent. The sūrah presents both, in sequence, in the same room, and then lets Solomon's response at verse 40 evaluate them.

And the offer **provides the occasion for Solomon's prayer**. Because the *ʿifrīt* claimed strength and trustworthiness for himself, and because the throne then arrived by a means neither volunteer's credentials can explain, Solomon's response is necessarily about God: *"This is of the Bounty of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful."* The *ʿifrīt*'s self-praise is what makes the king's God-praise necessary. Verse 39 exists so that verse 40 can happen.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:40

> **The one who had knowledge of the Book said, "I will bring it to thee in the blink of an eye." Then when he saw it set before him, he said, "This is of the Bounty of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful. And whosoever gives thanks, he gives thanks only for his own soul; and whosoever is ungrateful, truly my Lord is Self-Sufficient, Generous."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Longest and Most Important Verse in the Solomonic Narrative**

*Qāla'lladhī ʿindahu ʿilmun mina'l-kitābi anā ātīka bihī qabla an yartadda ilayka ṭarfuk* — "Said the one who had knowledge from the Book: 'I will bring it to you before your glance returns to you.'" Then: *fa-lammā raʾāhu mustaqirran ʿindahu qāla hādhā min faḍli rabbī li-yabluwanī a-ashkuru am akfuru wa man shakara fa-innamā yashkuru li-nafsihī wa man kafara fa-inna rabbī ghaniyyun karīm* — "Then when he saw it settled before him, he said: 'This is of the Bounty of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful. And whoever gives thanks, he gives thanks only for his own soul; and whoever is ungrateful — truly my Lord is Self-Sufficient, Generous.'"

The verse divides into three movements: the offer, the arrival, and the response. The first two are narrative; the third is doctrine, and it is among the most consequential statements about gratitude in the Qur'an.

**Alladhī ʿIndahu ʿIlmun Mina'l-Kitāb: The Man Described by What He Knows**

The second volunteer is not named. He is identified by a description: *the one who had knowledge from the Book*. Al-Ṭabarī states that he was a man, and the commentators record the name most commonly given — Āṣif ibn Barkhiyā — said to have been Solomon's cousin or his vizier, and to have known a Name of God by which supplication is answered. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī report this identification, and it is the position adopted by most of the classical exegetes.

Ibn Masʿūd is reported to have said that the man was Khiḍr — the mysterious servant of God who becomes Moses' teacher at 18:65–82, and whom the Qur'an describes there as *"one of Our servants upon whom We had bestowed mercy from Us, and whom We had taught knowledge from Our presence (ʿilman min ladunnā)."* The verbal parallel with our verse is striking: *ʿilmun mina'l-kitāb* and *ʿilman min ladunnā* both describe a knowledge given from a source beyond ordinary acquisition.

Al-Rāzī prefers a third reading, which he argues fits the context better: that the speaker was Solomon himself, addressing the *ʿifrīt*, and that the description "the one who had knowledge of the Book" refers to Solomon in the third person. On this reading Solomon says, "I will bring it to you before your glance returns to you" — to the jinn — and then, seeing the throne before him, offers the prayer of gratitude. Al-Rāzī's argument is that a prophet would be the most knowledgeable of the Book, and that the gratitude expressed implies that Solomon himself was the one who caused the throne's arrival.

The majority position — that the speaker is another man, Āṣif — is followed by Ibn Kathīr and by most of the tradition, and it has the advantage of preserving the comparison the sūrah set up at verse 38: two volunteers, two kinds of capacity. Al-Rāzī's reading is recorded and respected, and the Qur'an's own silence about the man's identity has kept the discussion open. What all readings agree on is the decisive point: the throne arrived by *knowledge*, not by strength.

**Mina'l-Kitāb: Which Book**

The commentators identify three possibilities for the Book referenced. Al-Rāzī records them: the Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*), of which the Qur'an speaks at 85:22 (*"Nay, it is a glorious Qur'an, in a Preserved Tablet"*) and at 56:77–80; the books of the prophets generally; or a book particular to Solomon, though what that book could be is not specified.

The phrase *min* — "from the Book" — is partitive, indicating a portion of knowledge drawn from a larger source. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī explain the operative content of that knowledge as a Name of God: a divine Name which, when invoked, causes the supplication to be answered. The tradition's concept here is that of *al-ism al-aʿẓam* — the Greatest Name — regarding which several ḥadīths are recorded:

> *"The Greatest Name of God is in these two verses: 'And your God is one God; there is no god but He, the Compassionate, the Merciful' (2:163), and the opening of Āl ʿImrān: 'Alif Lām Mīm. God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Self-Subsisting.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 1496; al-Tirmidhī 3478, from Asmāʾ bint Yazīd, graded ḥasan)

> *"A man supplicated: 'O God, I ask You by the fact that to You belongs the praise — there is no god but You, the Bestower, Originator of the heavens and the earth, O Possessor of Majesty and Honor, O Living, O Self-Subsisting' — and the Prophet ﷺ said: 'He has asked God by His Greatest Name, which, when He is asked by it, He gives, and when He is called upon by it, He answers.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 1495; al-Tirmidhī 3478, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The doctrine implied is that certain Names have a particular efficacy when invoked by one who knows them and is in a state fit to invoke them. This is not magic in the Qur'an's sense — *siḥr*, which verse 13 discussed and which operates by deception and by harm — but supplication answered, and the distinction between the two is the distinction the Qur'an insists upon throughout: the miracle is from God and serves His message; the sorcery is from human art and serves the sorcerer.

**Qabla An Yartadda Ilayka Ṭarfuk: The Blink of an Eye**

*"Before your glance returns to you."* The phrase is one of the Qur'an's most vivid temporal expressions, and the commentators analyze it with care. *Ṭarf* is from *ṭ-r-f*, the movement of the eyelid, the glance, the wink — the same root as *ṭaraf*, the extremity, and *ṭarafa*, to blink. *Yartadda* is Form VIII from *r-d-d*, to return, to be turned back.

Al-Ṭabarī records three explanations. For some it means the man would bring the throne in less time than it takes for a person within range of Solomon's vision to reach him. For others it means that in the time it takes Solomon to look as far as his sight reaches, the throne will appear before him. For still others, the throne sprang from the ground.

The dominant understanding is the simplest: the time of a single blink — the shortest interval human beings ordinarily use to mark immediacy. Arabic has a range of such expressions (*fī ṭarfati ʿayn*, *qabla an yarjiʿa ilayka ṭarfuk*), and the Qur'an uses this one to describe an event that occurred before the sentence finished being heard.

The comparison with verse 39 is now complete, and it is quantitative. The *ʿifrīt* offered the length of a council sitting — hours. The one with knowledge offered the length of a glance — a fraction of a second. The ratio between the two offers is the ratio the sūrah intends the reader to compute: strength operates in hours; knowledge operates in instants.

And the comparison is not between a fast jinn and a slow man. It is between the fastest thing Solomon's kingdom possesses by nature — an *ʿifrīt* of the jinn, strong and swift — and something else entirely, which the verse locates not in a species but in a *relation*: knowledge *from the Book*.

**Fa-Lammā Raʾāhu Mustaqirran ʿIndahu: The Arrival**

*"Then when he saw it settled before him."* The active participle *mustaqirran* is from *q-r-r*, to settle, to be fixed, to come to rest, to be established — the same root as *qarār*, a settled abode, and *waqāra*, dignity, and *qurrat al-ʿayn*, the delight of the eye (used in 20:40, 25:74, 28:9, 32:17). The Qur'an uses *mustaqarr* of the fixed resting place of the sun:

> *"And the sun runs to its fixed resting place (mustaqarrin lahā). That is the decree of the Mighty, the Knowing."* (Qur'an 36:38)

The word choice is precise. The throne did not appear in motion, did not materialize gradually, did not descend. It was *settled* — established, at rest, fixed in place, as though it had always been there. Al-Ṭabarī notes the commentators' discussion of whether it rose from the ground or was transported through the air; the Qur'an's own word tells us only the result: it was there, and it was stable.

The elision of the transport itself is significant. The Qur'an does not describe how the throne crossed two thousand miles. It describes the offer, and then the arrival. The mechanism is not narrated because the mechanism is not the point — and because, on the majority reading, the mechanism was a supplication answered, which is not a physical process the narrator could describe.

**Hādhā Min Faḍli Rabbī: The First Movement of the Response**

*"This is of the Bounty of my Lord."* Solomon's first words on seeing the throne are the same word he used at verse 16 — *faḍl*, unearned grace — with the same preposition (*min*) and the same possessive construction (*rabbī*). Al-Ṭabarī notes that the identification of the source is the first and most important act, and that the verse's structure depends on it: only a man who has already located his possessions in *faḍl* can locate this one there too.

The grammar is worth noting. *Hādhā min faḍli rabbī* is a nominal sentence with no verb — Arabic's most emphatic form of predication. Not "God has given me this" (verbal, event-describing) but "this is from the bounty of my Lord" (nominal, stating a fixed relation). The throne's ontological classification is asserted, not reported.

**Li-Yabluwanī A-Ashkuru Am Akfuru: The Second Movement — The Test**

*"To try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful."* The verse's most remarkable clause. Solomon does not interpret the miracle as a reward, a sign of favor, a confirmation of his rank, or an instrument against Bilqīs. He interprets it as a *test*.

*Li-yabluwanī* is from *b-l-w*, to test, to try, to prove — the same root as *balāʾ*, trial and affliction, and *ibtalā*, to examine. The Qur'an uses this vocabulary constantly of God's dealing with human beings:

> *"And We shall surely test you with something of fear and hunger, and loss of wealth and lives and fruits; but give glad tidings unto the patient."* (Qur'an 2:155)

> *"Verily We have made what is on the earth an adornment for it, that We may test them, [to see] which of them is best in deed."* (Qur'an 18:7)

> *"He Who created death and life, that He might test you, [to see] which of you is best in deed. And He is the Mighty, the Forgiving."* (Qur'an 67:2)

Al-Ṭabarī explains that Solomon understood such amazing blessings as the power to bring a throne from afar to contain within them a test of his sincerity. The point is a general principle of Islamic spirituality and it is stated nowhere more clearly than here: **blessings are tests**. The Qur'an makes the same point about wealth, health, children, and power:

> *"And know that your wealth and your children are but a trial, and that with God is a tremendous reward."* (Qur'an 8:28)

> *"As for man, when his Lord tests him and honors him and favors him, he says: 'My Lord has honored me.' And when He tests him and restricts his provision, he says: 'My Lord has humiliated me.'"* (Qur'an 89:15–16)

The last passage is the Qur'an's direct correction of the ordinary human reading of events. Prosperity is not honor and poverty is not humiliation; both are *tests*, and the test in prosperity is gratitude while the test in adversity is patience. Solomon, given the greatest demonstration of power available to a human being, correctly identifies it as the first kind of test.

**Man Shakara Fa-Innamā Yashkuru Li-Nafsihī: The Third Movement — Who Benefits From Gratitude**

*"And whoever gives thanks, he gives thanks only for his own soul."* The verse now generalizes from Solomon's own case to a principle, and the principle is one of the Qur'an's most repeated:

> *"And whoever gives thanks, he gives thanks only for his own soul; and whoever is ungrateful — truly my Lord is Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy."* — cf. the parallel at 31:12 and 39:7

> *"And whoever does righteousness, they prepare for themselves."* (Qur'an 30:44)

> *"If you do good, you do good for yourselves; and if you do evil, it is for it."* (Qur'an 17:7)

> *"And whoever strives, strives only for his own soul. Verily God is beyond need of the worlds."* (Qur'an 29:6)

> *"That is because God is not such as to change a favor He has conferred upon a people until they change what is in themselves."* (Qur'an 8:53)

The logic is precise and it is a logic of need. Gratitude does not benefit the one thanked, because God does not need it. Gratitude benefits the one who is grateful, because gratitude is a state of the soul that preserves it in correct relation to reality. The ungrateful person is not harming God; he is harming his own capacity to see, to receive, and to be happy.

Al-Rāzī develops this at length in his commentary: the benefit of *shukr* accrues to the servant in three ways. It preserves the blessing, because a blessing recognized is a blessing retained in perception, while a blessing unrecognized becomes invisible and then is lost without being mourned. It increases the blessing, as the Qur'an promises at 14:7: *"If you give thanks, I will surely increase you; and if you are ungrateful, verily My punishment is severe."* And it forms the soul in a disposition — receptivity — that makes further gifts possible.

The verse at 14:7 is the Qur'an's own commentary on verse 40, and the pairing of the two statements is worth setting side by side. God promises increase for gratitude and severe punishment for ingratitude; Solomon explains why — because the gratitude and the ingratitude are both, in the first instance, done *to oneself*.

**Fa-Inna Rabbī Ghaniyyun Karīm: The Two Names That Close the Verse**

*"And whoever is ungrateful — truly my Lord is Self-Sufficient, Generous."* The verse ends with two Divine Names, and their pairing is theologically loaded.

*Ghaniyy* — from *gh-n-y*, sufficiency, independence, freedom from need. Al-Ghaniyy is the One who needs nothing:

> *"O mankind, you are the poor toward God, and God is the Self-Sufficient, the Praiseworthy."* (Qur'an 35:15)

> *"Verily God is the Self-Sufficient; He does not need the worlds."* — cf. 29:6, 3:97, 31:26

Al-Ṭabarī notes that mentioning God's Self-Sufficiency here emphasizes that He has no need of gratitude from human beings and is neither helped by its presence nor harmed by its absence. This is the doctrine of divine *ghinā*, absolute independence, and it is what makes gratitude meaningful: a thanks that a needy recipient depends upon is a payment, not a thanks. God's gratitude-independence is what makes human gratitude free.

*Karīm* — from *k-r-m*, nobility, generosity, munificence. Al-Karīm is the One who gives without being asked, who gives to the ungrateful, who gives abundantly and without accounting. The Qur'an uses the Name at 23:116, 82:6, and 96:3 (*"Read! And thy Lord is the Most Generous (al-Akram), Who taught by the pen"*).

The pairing of *Ghaniyy* and *Karīm* at the end of a verse about ingratitude is one of the Qur'an's most exact theological statements. God does not need gratitude — *Ghaniyy*. And God continues to give to those who do not give it — *Karīm*. The ungrateful person therefore does not deprive God of anything, and does not even deprive himself of provision; he deprives himself of the *increase* promised at 14:7 and of the state of soul that gratitude produces.

Al-Qurṭubī observes that the verse thereby completes its argument in a single clause: the ingratitude harms the ingrate, the bounty continues regardless, and God's generosity is not a transaction. The commentators note further that *Karīm* here stands where other versions of the sentence use *Ḥamīd* (Praiseworthy) — as at 31:12 and 34:6 — and that Solomon's formulation emphasizes generosity over praiseworthiness because the context is a gift just received: the throne.

**The Verse as the Center of the Sūrah**

Verse 40 is the pivot of al-Naml, and the sūrah's structure confirms it. Everything before it moves toward the throne's arrival: the ant's warning (18), Solomon's prayer of gratitude (19), the hoopoe's report (22–26), the letter (28), the council (32–34), the gift (35–36), the threat (37), the request (38), the two offers (39–40). Everything after it moves away from the throne: the disguise (41), the recognition (42), the diagnosis (43), the pavilion and the submission (44).

And at the center stands the sentence that explains what the center is for: *"This is of the Bounty of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful."*

The sūrah named after an ant is a sūrah about gratitude, and its structural center is a king who has just been given everything and asks only whether he is being thanked correctly. Verse 19 prayed for the capacity to be held in gratitude (*awziʿnī an ashkura niʿmatak*). Verse 40 receives the greatest single blessing of the narrative and identifies it as a test of that capacity. The prayer has been answered by being tested, and the test is stated in the words the prayer used.

**Application: The Verse Every Recipient of Good Needs**

The practical reach of verse 40 is unlimited and immediate. Any person who has received anything — a job, a recovery, a child, a house, an idea, an unexpected kindness, a success — is standing where Solomon stood when he saw the throne settled before him, and has the same two options: *a-ashkuru am akfuru*.

The verse supplies three instruments for choosing correctly. First, the classification: name the thing as *faḍl* — as unearned bounty from a Lord, not as an achievement, not as luck, not as entitlement. Second, the interpretation: understand the thing as a test rather than as a reward, which changes what one does next (one becomes careful rather than expansive). Third, the accounting: recognize that the gratitude is for one's own soul, so that its absence is not a slight against God but a loss to oneself — which removes the last excuse, since no one chooses to lose.

The modern reader is surrounded by *mustaqirran ʿindahu* — things settled before him, arrived, installed, taken for granted: water in a tap, light in a room, information in a pocket, a body that has not yet failed. The verse's diagnostic question is whether any of it is still visible as *min faḍli rabbī*. The ant in verse 18 saw a marching army as a catastrophe and warned her neighbors. Solomon in verse 40 saw a transported throne as a test and thanked his Lord. The capacity to see is the same capacity in both cases, and the sūrah named after the ant is asking the reader whether he still has it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:41

> **He said, "Disguise her throne for her. We shall see if she is rightly guided or if she is among those who are not rightly guided."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Order to Alter the Throne**

*Qāla nakkirū lahā ʿarshahā nanẓur a-tahtadī am takūnu mina'lladhīna lā yahtadūn* — "He said: 'Alter her throne for her; we shall see whether she is guided, or whether she is among those who are not guided.'"

The verse contains a command and a purpose, and the purpose is stated with the same verb of deliberate observation that has run through the narrative: *nanẓur* — "we shall see." It is the fourth occurrence of *n-ẓar* in twenty verses (vv. 27, 28, 33, 35, and now 41), and each occurrence marks a moment at which a claim is to be tested by an arranged situation rather than by argument.

**Nakkirū: The Verb of Making Unrecognizable**

*Nakkirū* is the imperative plural of Form II from *n-k-r*, a root whose field is denial, non-recognition, and strangeness: *nakara*, to deny, to not recognize; *munkar*, the unrecognized, the disapproved, the morally objectionable; *nakīr*, denial; *nakirah*, an indefinite noun in grammar — one not yet identified. Form II, *nakkara*, means to make a thing *nakirah* — to render it unrecognized, to alter it so that its identity is uncertain.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that to "alter" the throne means to change it: to add some things and remove others. Al-Qurṭubī records the interpretation that it means to turn it upside down. The range of suggestions — additions, removals, inversion, repositioning — indicates that the commentators understood the alteration as partial rather than total, which the following verse confirms: the throne must remain recognizable enough that the question in verse 42 is a real question.

The command is addressed in the plural to an unspecified group — *nakkirū*, "you (all) alter" — and the commentators take it as addressed to his attendants or to the jinn in his service. The verse does not identify them, and the elision keeps the focus on the alteration itself rather than on who performed it.

**Why Alter It: The Purpose of the Test**

The stated purpose is *nanẓur a-tahtadī am takūnu mina'lladhīna lā yahtadūn* — "we shall see whether she is guided, or whether she is among those who are not guided." Al-Ṭabarī explains that *"we shall see"* implies waiting to see if the queen recognizes the throne, as it was meant to be a test for her; Ibn Kathīr records the same.

The commentators discuss what the test is testing, and their answers converge on the queen's intelligence and her openness to evidence. Ibn Kathīr notes at verse 42 that some say the question was a test of her intelligence: if she said "yes" or "no" outright, she would have been incorrect, because the throne was neither entirely the same nor entirely different. The test is therefore not a test of recognition but of *judgment* — the capacity to hold two possibilities open, to compare, and to arrive at a calibrated answer rather than a confident one.

This reading is confirmed by verse 42's outcome, in which Bilqīs answers *"kaʾannahu huwa"* — "it is as though it were it." Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both praise the answer as the correct one, and al-Rāzī considers it evidence of her intelligence: she neither asserted identity nor denied it, but reported the appearance accurately and left the determination open.

**The Phrase Mina'lladhīna Lā Yahtadūn: A Category the Sūrah Has Already Defined**

*"Or whether she is among those who are not guided."* The phrase is not new. Verse 24, the hoopoe's report, ended with exactly these words: *fa-hum lā yahtadūn* — "so they are not guided" — describing the sun-worshippers of Sabaʾ. Solomon is asking whether the queen belongs to the category the bird reported.

The verbal echo is deliberate and it compresses the whole intervening narrative. Twenty verses have passed: a letter, a council, a gift, a refusal, a threat, a transported throne. Solomon's question is the same question the hoopoe's report raised, and the throne's alteration is the instrument for answering it. If she sees her own seat of power standing in a foreign court, altered but recognizable, and she responds with judgment rather than with panic or denial, then she is not among those who are not guided — she is a person whose mind works on evidence.

The verb *tahtadī* is Form VIII from *h-d-y*, discussed at verse 2: to be guided, to find the way, to guide oneself. Form VIII carries a reflexive sense — the one guided participates in the guiding. Solomon is therefore not asking whether she has been converted; he is asking whether she is capable of finding the way, which is the precondition of being shown it. Verse 44 will report the arrival; verse 41 asks about the capacity.

**The Ethics of the Alteration**

A reader may reasonably ask whether altering another sovereign's throne, without her knowledge and for the purpose of testing her, is an act the Qur'an presents approvingly. The commentators address the question, and their answers rest on three considerations.

First, the throne was not taken as plunder. It was returned to its owner, unharmed in substance, and the alteration was reversible — as verse 44 will implicitly show, since the narrative never mentions the throne again after the queen's submission.

Second, the purpose was not deception but *disclosure*. A test that its subject could see through would not test anything; a test designed to reveal the subject's real capacities is exactly what Solomon says it is (*nanẓur* — we shall see). The Qur'an's own vocabulary for wrongful deception is different and is used elsewhere: *khadaʿa*, to deceive (2:9), *gharra*, to delude (31:33, 57:14), *zayyana*, to make evil seem fair (v. 4). None of these words appears here.

Third, and decisively, the outcome justified the means in the sense the tradition requires: the test produced information that led to a real good.
