Bilqīs's answer at verse 42 disclosed a mind capable of calibrated judgment; her experience at verse 44 produced her submission; and a nation stopped worshiping the sun. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the whole episode is presented in the Qur'an without censure of Solomon at any point, and that the narrative's own evaluation of his conduct is positive throughout.

There is a general principle here about tests and the authority to administer them. A test is legitimate when the tester has the standing to test, the test measures something real, the subject is not harmed by it, and the result is used for the subject's benefit. Solomon meets all four conditions; a person who meets none of them and administers a "test" to a subordinate, a spouse, or a friend is not doing what Solomon did.

**The Sufi Reading: The Throne as the Soul**

Ibn ʿAjībah, as recorded in the source commentary, interprets the encounter between Solomon and Bilqīs on a spiritual level as an allegory of the relation between a spiritual master and a disciple, and he notes that in the spiritual life one relinquishes one's throne — which symbolizes the soul — and it is transfigured by the spiritual master into something new, but not completely different.

The allegory preserves the plain sense's essential structure. The throne is what a person sits upon, what supports her authority, what she identifies as hers. To have it altered — some things added, some removed, perhaps inverted — and then to be asked "is this your throne?" is a description of what happens to a soul under genuine spiritual direction. The disciple is shown herself, changed, and asked to recognize the change without either claiming it as her own achievement or denying that it is her.

Bilqīs's answer — "it is as though it were it" — is, on this reading, the correct answer of a disciple: neither the pride of "yes, this is what I have become," nor the denial of "no, this is not me," but the accurate report of an appearance held in suspension until the master discloses the reality. The commentaries on Ibn ʿArabī's *Fuṣūṣ* note that Bilqīs was taught here a lesson about appearances: about seeing what she believed should not have been there (her throne) and about not seeing what was in reality there (glass, not water) — a kind of allegory for the challenge of overcoming appearances to reach the true nature of things, which is a goal of the spiritual life.

The two lessons — the misplaced throne and the misperceived water — form a single pair, and verse 44 will complete it. Between them, the queen learns the two things a person must learn to see anything at all: that what is present may not be where it should be, and that what appears may not be what it is.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:42

> **Then when she came, it was said, "Is your throne like this?" She said, "It seems the same." [Solomon said], "And we were given knowledge before her and we were submitters,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arrival of the Queen, and the Question Put to Her**

*Falammā jāʾat qī-la a-hākadhā ʿarshuki qālat kaʾannahu huwa* — "Then when she came, it was said: 'Is your throne like this?' She said: 'It is as though it were it.'"

The verse reports the queen's arrival without narrative transition — *fa-lammā jāʾat*, "then when she came" — in the same compressed style as verse 29, where the scene opened with her already speaking to her council. The Qur'an's method is to omit the journey and present the encounter, and the elision is itself meaningful: the travel from Yemen to Palestine, the reception, the protocol, and the approach are all irrelevant to what the verse is doing, which is putting a question to a mind.

**A-Hākadhā ʿArshuki: A Question Designed to Be Answered Carefully**

*"Is your throne like this?"* The interrogative is formed with *a-* followed by *hākadhā* — "like this, thus" — and the word order places the demonstrative before the noun: literally, "is thus your throne?" Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī note the construction's effect: the question does not ask "is this your throne?" which would demand a yes or no about identity. It asks "is your throne *like this*?" which demands a comparison and permits a calibrated answer.

Ibn Kathīr records the interpretation that this was a test of her intelligence: if she said "yes" or "no" flatly, she would have been wrong, because the throne was neither entirely the same nor entirely different. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *"it seems the same"* indicates she was in doubt, and the doubt was appropriate: the evidence genuinely supported doubt.

The question is therefore not a trick. A trick question is one in which the correct answer is concealed by the form of the question; this is a question in which the correct answer is *uncertainty honestly reported*, and the form of the question permits it. Solomon has asked the only question that could reveal whether the woman in front of him can think.

**Kaʾannahu Huwa: The Answer**

*"It is as though it were it."* The Arabic *kaʾannahu huwa* is formed from *ka-anna* — "as if, as though" — plus the pronoun *huwa*. The construction expresses resemblance with a degree of conviction: not "it is it," not "it is not it," but "it is as though it were it."

The commentators' evaluation of the answer is uniformly admiring. Al-Qurṭubī records that she correctly evaluated the situation by saying the throne "seemed" like her very throne. Al-Rāzī develops the reasoning: she was shown an object resembling the one she had left behind, two thousand miles away, in a court she had just entered, and she had three possible responses. She could have denied the resemblance and preserved her own framework (this cannot be my throne, therefore it is not). She could have asserted identity and abandoned judgment (this is my throne, therefore something impossible has happened). Or she could have reported the appearance accurately and suspended the conclusion — which is what she did.

The third response is the only one that is both honest and rational, and it requires a specific intellectual virtue: the capacity to hold a perception and a belief in tension without resolving the tension prematurely. Modern epistemology has a name for the failure mode Bilqīs avoided — motivated reasoning, the unconscious adjustment of conclusions to fit prior commitments. The Qur'an's diagnosis of the same failure is verse 14: *they rejected them, though their souls were convinced of them, wrongfully and exultantly.* Bilqīs's answer is the opposite of verse 14 in a single phrase.

**The Queen's Prior Knowledge**

There is a further element in her answer that the commentators note. Bilqīs arrived knowing that her throne was in her palace — she had left it there, presumably guarded, in a kingdom that had not been invaded. When she saw a throne resembling it in Solomon's court, she had to reconcile two facts: her own knowledge of where her throne was, and the evidence in front of her.

Her answer reconciles them by declining to reconcile them. "It is as though it were it" accepts the evidence without abandoning the prior belief. It is the answer of someone who understands that one of her two data points must be wrong and does not yet know which. This is a genuinely difficult intellectual posture to hold, and most people cannot hold it: they resolve the tension immediately, in whichever direction their commitments point.

The Qur'an's presentation of the moment is thus a small masterclass in the epistemology of the unexpected. When confronted with evidence that contradicts what you know, the correct first response is not to accept or reject but to *report the appearance accurately and wait for more information.* Bilqīs waits. Verse 44 will supply the information.

**Wa Ūtīnā ʿIlman Qablahā Wa Kunnā Muslimīn: The Second Half of the Verse**

*"And we were given knowledge before her, and we were submitters."* The attribution of this clause is disputed, and the source commentary records both positions.

The majority reading assigns it to Solomon: having heard her answer, he says that his party had been given knowledge before she had — knowledge of God, of the truth of his message, of the reality behind the appearance — and that they were already submitters. Al-Ṭabarī records this reading.

A minority reading, recorded by al-Zamakhsharī and discussed by al-Rāzī, assigns the clause to Bilqīs as a continuation of her speech: she acknowledges that a certain level of recognition of God's power and of Solomon's prophethood had reached her before she was shown the wonders of her transformed throne and the glass pavilion, and that she had in a sense been held back from the fullness of faith by the erroneous religion she practiced — which verse 43 then states explicitly.

The second reading connects verse 42 to verse 43 with unusual smoothness, since verse 43 says *"while that which she worshipped apart from God barred her"* — a clause that fits naturally after an acknowledgment of partial belief. Al-Zamakhsharī prefers it for this reason.

The first reading has the support of the majority and of the narrative's focus. On either reading, the clause makes the same two claims: that knowledge preceded the demonstration, and that submission accompanied the knowledge. Both claims are the sūrah's own thesis, and both are stated in the plural — *ūtīnā*, "we were given"; *kunnā*, "we were" — which on Solomon's reading includes his court and on Bilqīs's reading includes her people.

**"We Were Given Knowledge Before Her": The Priority of Knowledge**

The phrase *qablahā* — "before her" — establishes a temporal priority that the sūrah has been arguing since verse 15. Knowledge comes first; the demonstration comes second; the demonstration confirms the knowledge rather than creating it.

This is the Qur'an's consistent account of how belief works. The signs do not produce faith in a mind that has none; they confirm and complete a faith whose ground is already laid. Hence verse 2 of this sūrah — the Book is *"a guidance and glad tidings for the believers"*; hence 47:17 — *"and as for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance"*; hence the hoopoe's report at verse 22, which presupposes that Solomon's court already possessed the knowledge by which the report could be evaluated.

Bilqīs is being shown a throne that has crossed a continent because she has already demonstrated, in verses 32–35, that she possesses the intellectual and moral equipment to interpret what she is shown. The demonstration is not addressed to her ignorance; it is addressed to her judgment. And her judgment, tested at verse 42, produces the answer that makes verse 44 possible.

**"And We Were Submitters": The Second Claim**

*Kunnā muslimīn* — "we were submitters." The verb is the past tense of "to be" with the active participle, denoting a settled state: we were, and had been, people of submission. The word is the same as the letter's demand at verse 31 — *waʾtūnī muslimīn* — and its appearance here marks the point at which the demand has been met.

On Solomon's reading, the statement identifies his party as already what the letter asked her to become, which makes the demonstration not an imposition but an invitation to join an existing condition. On Bilqīs's reading, it is her own acknowledgment that the movement toward submission had already begun before the throne was shown to her.

Either way the verse records the same structural fact: submission preceded the miracle. The miracle did not create submitters; it addressed people who were already becoming them. This is the sūrah's answer to a question the reader has been entitled to ask since verse 35 — why did Bilqīs respond to evidence at all, when Pharaoh did not? Because she was already, in some measure, one of the *muslimīn* the letter had called for. Verse 44 will make it explicit.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:43

> **while that which she worshipped apart from God barred her; verily she was from a disbelieving people."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnosis of an Almost-Believer**

*Wa ṣaddahā mā kānat taʿbudu min dūni Llāh innahā kānat min qawmin kāfirīn* — "And what she had been worshiping, apart from God, barred her; verily she was of a disbelieving people."

This verse is the sūrah's explanation of a delay. Bilqīs has received a letter, consulted her council, tested a king, traveled to his court, and answered a question about her own throne with a calibration that the commentators praise as the mark of intelligence. She is, by every indication, on the threshold. Verse 43 explains why she has not crossed it: something is barring her, and the verse names it.

**Ṣaddahā: The Verb of Obstruction**

*Ṣaddahā* is from *ṣ-d-d*, to bar, to turn away, to obstruct — the identical verb the hoopoe used of Satan at verse 24: *fa-ṣaddahum ʿani'l-sabīl*, "and he barred them from the way." The verbal echo is exact and it is the key to the verse.

At verse 24 the agent of barring was Satan, and the object barred from was *al-sabīl*, the way. At verse 43 the agent of barring is *what she had been worshiping apart from God*, and the object barred from — supplied by the context — is the same way. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the queen's worship of the sun prevented her from worshiping God properly. The mechanism is the same in both verses: a practice in which a person is invested prevents the perception that would end the practice.

**Mā Kānat Taʿbudu: The Habitual Past**

*"What she had been worshiping"* — the construction *kānat taʿbudu* is the imperfect of "to be" followed by the imperfect verb, which in Arabic denotes a continuing past state or a settled habit: she was one who worshiped, she had been worshiping, this was her ongoing practice.

The habitual aspect is essential to the verse's diagnosis. What barred Bilqīs was not a single act of sun-worship but a *practice* — something done repeatedly, in public, with a nation, over years. Al-Rāzī develops the point in his discussion of verse 24: part of the sin of Sabaʾ was that they were open about their illicit activity and did not seek to keep it from view. A public, collective, habitual practice creates a structure of incentives around a person: priests, temples, festivals, a calendar, a national identity, an economy, a body of learning, and the regard of everyone who shares it. To abandon such a practice is not merely to change a belief; it is to dismantle a world.

This is why the verse says the worship *barred* her rather than that she *resisted*. The obstruction was structural, not merely volitional. She was not refusing; she was being held back by something she had built around herself over a lifetime.

**Min Dūni Llāh: The Formula of Misplaced Worship**

The phrase *min dūni Llāh* — "apart from God" — is the same formula the hoopoe used at verse 24, and it is the Qur'an's standard designation for whatever occupies the position that belongs to God alone. Its analysis was given at verse 24 above; here it acquires a specific application.

What Bilqīs worshiped *apart from God* was the sun — the most impressive visible object in the sky, the source of light, heat, seasons, agriculture, and the calendar. The commentators note that sun-worship is, of all forms of paganism, the most reasonable on its own terms: the object is real, powerful, regular, universally beneficial, and beyond human manipulation. It is not a carved stone. It is not an ancestor. It is the largest, most reliable, most evidently necessary thing in the observable world.

And that is precisely why it barred her. An obvious error is easy to abandon. A *plausible* error is almost impossible to abandon, because every consideration that recommends it must be reconsidered at once. Bilqīs's sun was not a superstition she could shrug off; it was the best explanation her civilization had for the order of the world. Leaving it required not a change of habit but a change of cosmology — and verse 44 will show what that took.

**Innahā Kānat Min Qawmin Kāfirīn: The Second Bar**

*"Verily she was of a disbelieving people."* The clause adds a second factor to the diagnosis, and the two factors are complementary. The first — what she worshiped — is a practice. The second — the people she belonged to — is a community.

*Kāfirīn* is the active participle of *kafara*, from *k-f-r*, to cover, to conceal, to bury — the same root as *kaffārah*, expiation (a covering of sin), and *kāfir*, one who covers over what he knows. A farmer is called *kuffār* in the Qur'an (57:20) because he covers the seed with soil. The *kāfir* is one who covers over the truth: not one who has never encountered it, but one who has encountered it and buried it.

The Qur'an uses this word of the disbelievers consistently and always with the implication of covering rather than of mere absence:

> *"And they denied them — though their souls were convinced of them — wrongfully and exultantly."* (Qur'an 27:14)

> *"Those unto whom We gave the Book recognize it as they recognize their own sons, yet verily a party among them conceal the truth while they know it."* (Qur'an 2:146)

Bilqīs's belonging to *a disbelieving people* is therefore not a statement about her personal guilt alone. It is a statement about the difficulty of her position: she is one person, embedded in a collective practice of covering, whose entire apparatus of priests, courtiers, and subjects shares the practice and depends on it. To uncover the truth for herself would be to uncover it against them.

**The Two Bars, and What Removed Them**

The verse thus identifies two obstructions: a habitual practice of worship directed at a plausible object, and a community invested in that practice. Both are real, both are structural, and neither can be removed by argument. The sūrah knows this, which is why the removal in verse 44 does not occur through argument.

What removes them is an experience. Bilqīs is told to enter a pavilion; she sees a floor she takes for water; she bares her legs to wade; she is told it is crystal. In that moment she discovers two things at once — that she had been wrong about what she was seeing, and that the man who built the pavilion knew what she did not. Verse 44 reports her response: a confession of self-wronging and an act of submission.

The commentators on Ibn ʿArabī's *Fuṣūṣ* note exactly this, as recorded in the source: Bilqīs was taught a lesson about appearances — about seeing what she believed should not have been there (her throne) and about not seeing what was in reality there (glass, not water). The two experiences together broke the epistemic confidence on which her sun-worship rested. If her eyes could be wrong about a floor, they could be wrong about a sun.

**The General Law of the Verse**

Verse 43 states a law about human belief that operates far beyond its narrative setting, and the law is worth stating plainly: **what a person has been worshiping apart from God bars him from God, and the people he belongs to bar him with it.**

The first half applies to every object of ultimate concern that is not God. The Qur'an's own enumeration is comprehensive:

> *"Have thou considered him who has taken his caprice (hawāhu) as his god?"* (Qur'an 25:43)

> *"Hast thou seen him who takes his own desire as his god, and God leads him astray knowingly, and sets a seal upon his hearing and his heart, and places a covering over his sight? Who then will guide him after God? Will you not then take heed?"* (Qur'an 45:23)

The person who has made his appetite his god is barred by it in exactly the way Bilqīs was barred by the sun — not by a doctrine but by a practice, repeated daily, that occupies the position where the perception of God would otherwise occur.

The second half applies to every community of practice. The Qur'an's account of why people persist in error is overwhelmingly social:

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'Follow what God has sent down,' they say: 'Nay, we follow what we found our fathers upon.' What! Even though their fathers had no understanding of anything, and were not guided?"* (Qur'an 2:170)

> *"They said: 'Nay, we found our fathers doing thus.'"* (Qur'an 26:74; cf. 43:22–23)

> *"And thus We sent thee not a warner unto a town, but its wealthy ones said: 'Verily we disbelieve in that with which you have been sent.'"* (Qur'an 34:34)

Bilqīs was not merely a sun-worshipper; she was the queen of sun-worshippers, and the cost of leaving was the cost of leaving at the head of a nation. That she did leave, in verse 44, and that her leaving is reported as an individual act — *"My Lord! Surely I have wronged myself"* — is the reason the Qur'an gives her story so much space. It is the sūrah's demonstration that the two bars can be removed, and that they are removed not by argument but by the collapse of an appearance.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:44

> **It was said unto her, "Enter the pavilion." But when she saw it, she supposed it to be an expanse of water and bared her legs. He said, "Verily it is a pavilion paved with crystal." She said, "My Lord! Surely I have wronged myself, and I submit with Solomon to God, Lord of the worlds."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene the Whole Sūrah Has Been Building Toward**

*Qīla laha'dkhulī'l-ṣarḥa fa-lammā raʾat-hu ḥasibat-hu lujjatan wa kashafat ʿan sāqayhā qāla innahu ṣarḥun mumarradun min qawārīra qālat rabbi innī ẓalamtu nafsī wa aslamtu maʿa Sulaymāna li'Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn* — "It was said unto her: 'Enter the pavilion.' Then when she saw it, she supposed it to be an expanse of water, and she bared her two legs. He said: 'Verily it is a pavilion paved with crystal.' She said: 'My Lord! Truly I have wronged myself, and I submit with Solomon to God, Lord of the worlds.'"

Verse 44 is the longest verse in al-Naml and its narrative climax. It contains four speeches — a command, a misperception, a correction, and a confession — and it moves a queen from the threshold described in verse 43 to the submission that is the sūrah's central event.

**Al-Ṣarḥ: The Pavilion**

*Ṣarḥ* is from *ṣ-r-ḥ*, and it denotes a lofty, open building — a pavilion, a palace, a courtyard structure, an exposed and elevated edifice. The commentators interpret it as a palace or a fortress, and al-Qurṭubī records that some describe it as a raised structure with a floor of glass over water. The word appears only twice in the Qur'an, both in this verse, and its sense of *openness* is essential: a *ṣarḥ* is a building one enters and sees through, not one that encloses.

**Lujjah: The Expanse of Water**

*Ḥasibat-hu lujjatan* — "she supposed it to be an expanse of water." *Lujjah* is from *l-j-j*, and it denotes deep water, a wide expanse of it, the sea's depth — the same root as *lajj*, the din and commotion of waves and voices. The word conveys not a puddle but a body of water deep enough that one must wade or be carried.

*Ḥasibat-hu* — "she reckoned it, supposed it, took it to be" — is from *ḥ-s-b*, to calculate, to reckon, to suppose, to count. The same root gives *ḥisāb*, the reckoning of the Day of Judgment, and *ḥasaba*, to consider something to be the case. The Qur'an uses this verb repeatedly for a mistaken reckoning:

> *"Do not suppose (la-taḥsabanna) those who rejoice in what they have brought, and love to be praised for what they have not done — do not suppose that they are in safety from the punishment."* (Qur'an 3:188)

> *"And let not those who disbelieve suppose (yaḥsabanna) that We give them respite only that it may be better for them."* (Qur'an 3:178)

The verb's appearance here is precise. Bilqīs did not *see* water; she *reckoned* water. The verse distinguishes the perception from the judgment built upon it, and the distinction is the whole of the scene: her eyes reported a surface, and her mind supplied an interpretation, and the interpretation was wrong.

**Wa Kashafat ʿAn Sāqayhā: The Action That Revealed the Error**

*"And she bared her two legs."* The verb *kashafa* is from *k-sh-f*, to uncover, to reveal, to remove a covering — the same root as *kashf*, unveiling, and *kashafa al-ḍurr*, "He removed the harm," used of God at 6:17 and 10:107. The action is practical and entirely reasonable: a woman about to wade through deep water lifts her skirts.

The commentators note that the baring of the legs is what disclosed the error to her. It is also, in the narrative's economy, what disclosed her humanity: she acted on a false supposition, in public, in front of a foreign court, and the action was visible. Al-Qurṭubī records the folkloric material attached to this detail — that Solomon's jinn told him the mother of Bilqīs was a jinn with the legs of an ass, and that this ruse was prepared to expose her legs; and that after the incident, according to other legends, Solomon fell in love with Bilqīs and married her.

Ibn Kathīr, echoing al-Rāzī's sentiment regarding verse 35, sees little value in this folkloric material, which originated in Arab legends and in the oral traditions of various converts to Islam from Judaism and Christianity. The scholarly position is the one established at verse 35: the Qur'an's silence about the motive for the pavilion and about what happened afterward is a silence the exegete should respect. The reports are *isrāʾīliyyāt*, neither confirmed nor contradicted by revelation, and Ibn Kathīr's judgment on them is that they add nothing to the verse's meaning.

What the Qur'an does report is the correction and the confession, and they are sufficient.

**Ṣarḥun Mumarradun Min Qawārīra: The Correction**

*"Verily it is a pavilion paved with crystal."* *Mumarrad* is from *m-r-d*, and it denotes something smoothed, leveled, made even and polished — the same root as *amrad*, smooth, and *marīd*, rebellious (one who has thrown off restraint, i.e., made smooth of constraint). Applied to a floor, *mumarrad* means perfectly leveled and polished.

*Qawārīr* is the plural of *qārūrah*, a vessel of glass — from *q-r-r*, to settle, to be fixed (the same root as *mustaqirran* at verse 40). Glass is so called because the molten material settles and becomes fixed in its form. The Qur'an uses the word of the floor here and of the vessels of Paradise:

> *"And there shall be circulated among them vessels of silver and cups of crystal (qawārīr), crystal of silver — they have measured them with exact measure."* (Qur'an 76:15–16)

The correction is delivered in six words and it performs three operations at once. It identifies the material (crystal, not water). It identifies the object (a pavilion, not a pool). And it implicitly identifies the source of the error (your eyes reported a surface, and the surface was polished and transparent over water — or over a floor made to look like water).

Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī all note the report that part of the illusion was that there were actually fish swimming beneath the crystal, which is what made the floor look like water. The Qur'an does not state this, and the commentators report it as an explanation of the mechanism rather than as revelation.

**The Epistemological Content of the Scene**

The pavilion episode is one of the Qur'an's most concentrated statements about the reliability of perception, and the commentators on Ibn ʿArabī's *Fuṣūṣ* identified its structure exactly: Bilqīs was taught about appearances — about seeing what she believed should not have been there (her throne, at verse 42) and about not seeing what was in reality there (crystal, mistaken for water).

The two lessons are the two fundamental errors of perception. The first is *seeing what is not there* — or rather, seeing something that is there and believing it cannot be, so that the mind rejects the evidence. The second is *not seeing what is there* — seeing a surface and assigning it the wrong substance, so that the mind accepts a false interpretation of true data.

Between them, the two episodes dismantle the epistemic foundation of Bilqīs's religion. Her sun-worship rested on the reliability of the visible: the sun is what it appears to be, the largest and most powerful thing in the sky, and what appears greatest is greatest. Verse 42 showed her that something impossible could be present without her knowing it. Verse 44 showed her that something present could be absent from her perception entirely. If her eyes could be wrong about a floor two feet in front of her, they could be wrong about a star ninety-three million miles away.

This is the argument the Qur'an makes explicitly elsewhere, and verse 44 performs it:

> *"And upon the earth are signs for those possessing certitude, and within your own selves. Do you not then see?"* (Qur'an 51:20–21)

> *"We shall show them Our signs upon the horizons and within themselves, until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth."* (Qur'an 41:53)

**Rabbi Innī Ẓalamtu Nafsī: The Confession**

*"My Lord! Truly I have wronged myself."* The queen's response begins with the same address Solomon used at verse 19 — *rabbi*, "my Lord" — and moves immediately to a confession.

*Ẓalamtu nafsī* — "I have wronged myself" — uses the verb discussed at verse 14, *ẓalama*, to place a thing where it does not belong. The object of the wrong is *nafsī*, myself. The formula is the Qur'an's standard confession, and it appears in the mouths of its most significant figures:

> *"They said: 'Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves (ẓalamnā anfusanā). If Thou dost not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we shall surely be among the losers.'"* (Qur'an 7:23 — Adam and his wife)

> *"And [Jonah] called out in the darknesses: 'There is no god but Thou! Glory be to Thee! Verily I was among the wrongdoers (mina'l-ẓālimīn).'"* (Qur'an 21:87)

> *"And Moses said: 'My Lord! Truly I have wronged myself (ẓalamtu nafsī), so forgive me.' And He forgave him."* (Qur'an 28:16)

Bilqīs's confession places her in this company, and the placement is significant. She speaks the words of Adam, of Jonah, and of Moses — the words that the Qur'an presents as the correct human response to the recognition of one's own error. The Prophet ﷺ taught these words as a daily supplication, in the report recorded by Muslim:

> *"The best supplication is the supplication on the Day of ʿArafah, and the best of what I and the prophets before me have said is: 'There is no god but God, alone, without partner; to Him belongs the dominion and to Him belongs the praise, and He has power over all things.'"* (al-Tirmidhī 3585)

and the duʿāʾ of Dhū'l-Nūn, on which al-Tirmidhī records:

> *"The supplication of Dhū'l-Nūn, when he supplicated in the belly of the whale: 'There is no god but Thou! Glory be to Thee! Verily I was among the wrongdoers' — no Muslim man supplicates with it for anything, ever, but that God answers him."* (al-Tirmidhī 3505, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The confession's first word is also its most important: *Rabbi*. Before verse 44, the queen's recorded speech addressed her council (*yā ayyuha'l-malaʾ*, twice) and her envoys. Her first recorded address to God is *my Lord* — and it is followed immediately by an admission of self-wronging. The sequence is the sequence of conversion in the Qur'an: recognition of the Lord, recognition of the self.

**Wa Aslamtu Maʿa Sulaymāna Li'Llāhi Rabbi'l-ʿĀlamīn: The Submission**

*"And I submit, with Solomon, to God, Lord of the worlds."* The verb *aslamtu* is Form IV from *s-l-m*, the same word Solomon's letter demanded at verse 31 (*waʾtūnī muslimīn*) and that verse 42 reported of his party (*wa kunnā muslimīn*). The demand made twenty verses earlier has now been met, in the same vocabulary, by the person to whom it was addressed.

Three features of the phrasing have been carefully examined.

**Maʿa Sulaymān — "with Solomon."** The preposition *maʿa* indicates accompaniment, not subordination in essence. She does not submit *to* Solomon; she submits *with* him — joining him in an act directed elsewhere. Ibn ʿArabī's observation in the *Fuṣūṣ*, recorded in the source commentary, draws the theological consequence: the submission of Bilqīs was superior to that of Pharaoh, because she submitted to the Lord of the worlds without qualification, whereas he submitted — though too late according to many theologians — to "the Lord of Moses and Aaron" (7:122; 26:48). That is, Bilqīs understood what God was in Himself, whereas Pharaoh's understanding was limited to the power of God as manifested through some of His prophets.

The comparison is exact and it is devastating for Pharaoh. At the moment of drowning he said: *"I believe that there is no god save Him in Whom the Children of Israel believe"* (10:90), and the magicians had said *"we believe in the Lord of Moses and Aaron"* (26:48) — formulations that locate God through a people. Bilqīs says *li'Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn* — to God, Lord of the worlds — a formulation that locates God in Himself and universalizes His lordship. And she says it while alive, while in possession of her kingdom, and while it costs her something.

**Li'Llāh — "to God."** The preposition *lām* indicates the direction and the recipient of the submission. She submits *to* God, not to Solomon, not to a nation, not to a political arrangement. The *maʿa* and the *li* together produce a precise theological statement: I join Solomon in the act, and the act is directed to God.

**Rabbi'l-ʿĀlamīn — "Lord of the worlds."** The phrase is the Qur'an's most comprehensive description of God's lordship, and it is the second half of the Fātiḥah's opening: *al-ḥamdu li'Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn* (1:2). *ʿĀlamīn* is the sound plural of *ʿālam*, a world, a realm of beings — used comprehensively of everything other than God: the world of humans, of jinn, of angels, of animals, of plants, of the unseen. Al-Rāzī explains that the term includes every genus of created being.

The phrase's appearance in Bilqīs's mouth is the completion of the sūrah's argument about scale, and it is the second time the phrase has appeared in this narrative: verse 8 had the voice from the fire say *"And glory be to God, Lord of the worlds."* Moses heard it at the burning bush; Bilqīs says it in a glass pavilion. Between those two moments the sūrah has presented an ant, a bird, a throne, and a queen — and all of them are inside *al-ʿālamīn*.

The woman who had been worshiping the sun — a single object within one of the worlds — now addresses the Lord of all of them. That is the conversion, stated in four words.

**What the Queen Lost and What She Gained**

The narrative does not tell us what happened to Bilqīs's kingdom after verse 44. The commentators report that she returned to Sabaʾ and ruled as a believer, and the folkloric traditions add a marriage; Ibn Kathīr treats such material with the reserve recorded above. The Qur'an's own silence is deliberate and it leaves the reader with the one fact the verse establishes: a sovereign surrendered her religion, and the Qur'an presents the surrender as the correct decision and as an act of intelligence.

Sūrah Sabaʾ supplies the epilogue, describing what happened to the civilization that did not give thanks:

> *"Verily there was for Sabaʾ a sign in their dwelling place: two gardens on the right hand and the left... But they turned away, so We sent upon them the flood of the dam, and We replaced their two gardens with two gardens of bitter fruit and tamarisk and a few lote-trees. That We repaid them for their ingratitude. And do We repay any save the ungrateful?"* (Qur'an 34:15–17)

Al-Naml and Sabaʾ together present the two possible outcomes of the same civilization: the queen who recognized *faḍl* and submitted, and the people who turned away and lost the gardens. The hinge between them is the word Solomon used at verse 40 and the word 34:17 ends with: gratitude and ingratitude.

**The Verse's Standing Application**

Verse 44 is the Qur'an's fullest account of how a person changes. Not by argument — Bilqīs had arguments available from verse 26 onward and did not act on them. Not by force — Solomon's threat at verse 37 was never executed. Not by interest — the gift at verse 35 was refused and the refusal did not convert her.

She changed when she was shown that she could be wrong about something immediately in front of her, and when she had the honesty to say so out loud. The two elements are both necessary and neither is common. Most people are shown their errors constantly and do not admit them; a few admit errors but are never shown any. Bilqīs was shown one and admitted it, in a single verse, in four words — and the sūrah named after an ant ends its longest narrative with a queen saying *I have wronged myself.*

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:45

> **And indeed We sent to Thamūd their brother Ṣāliḥ, [saying], "Worship God!" And behold, they were two groups, quarreling.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Case Study: A Nation Divided**

*Wa laqad arsalnā ilā Thamūda akhāhum Ṣāliḥan ani'budū Llāha fa-idhā hum farīqāni yakhtaṣimūn* — "And verily We sent to Thamūd their brother Ṣāliḥ, [saying]: 'Worship God!' And behold — they were two factions, quarreling."

With verse 45 the sūrah leaves the Solomonic narrative and turns to its third case study. The structure of al-Naml's middle section is now visible: Moses and Pharaoh (vv. 7–14), a sovereign and a subject who was certain and refused; Solomon and Bilqīs (vv. 15–44), a sovereign who was tested and submitted; and now Ṣāliḥ and Thamūd (vv. 45–53), a community divided against itself. The three cases present three outcomes of the same demand — *worship God* — and the sūrah will add a fourth, Lot (vv. 54–58), before turning to its theological conclusion.

**Thamūd: The People and Their Place**

Thamūd was an ancient Arabian people whose remains were known to the Qur'an's first hearers, and whose name appears in pre-Islamic sources including Assyrian records from the eighth century BCE and the classical geographers. They inhabited the region of al-Ḥijr — modern Madāʾin Ṣāliḥ, in northwestern Arabia between Madinah and Tabūk — and they are described in the Qur'an as carvers of houses in rock:

> *"And [remember] the Thamūd, when their brother Ṣāliḥ said unto them: 'Will you not revere [God]? ... And you carve houses in the mountains with great skill.'"* (Qur'an 26:141, 149)

> *"And as for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance, so the blast of the humiliating punishment seized them for what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 41:17)

> *"And [We sent] unto Thamūd their brother Ṣāliḥ. He said: 'O my people, worship God; you have no god other than Him. There has come unto you a clear proof from your Lord: this is the she-camel of God, a sign for you. So let her eat in God's earth, and do not harm her, lest a painful punishment seize you.'"* (Qur'an 7:73)

The Qur'an narrates the Thamūd story at length in five places: 7:73–79, 11:61–68, 26:141–159, 27:45–53, and 54:23–31. Each account emphasizes a different aspect — 7 the demand and the she-camel, 11 the argument with the notables, 26 the dialogue in the standard prophetic formula, 54 the hamstringing and the cry — and al-Naml's account is distinctive in emphasizing the *internal division* of the community and the *conspiracy* of its nine men.

The Prophet ﷺ passed through al-Ḥijr on the expedition to Tabūk, and al-Bukhārī and Muslim record his instruction:

> *"When you pass by the dwellings of those who wronged themselves, do not enter upon them except while weeping, lest what befell them befall you."* (al-Bukhārī 3379; Muslim 2980)

And in another report from Ibn ʿUmar:

> *"The people descended with the Messenger of God ﷺ at al-Ḥijr, and they kneaded dough and put their cooking-pots on the fire. Then the Messenger of God ﷺ ordered them to pour out the cooking-pots and feed the dough to the camels, and he ordered them to move on and camp at the well from which the she-camel used to drink."* (al-Bukhārī 3378; Muslim 2981)

The reports establish that the site was known, visited, and treated by the Prophet ﷺ as a real place with a real history — which is why the refrain *"behold how was the end"* at verse 51 is addressed to people who could go and look.

**Akhāhum Ṣāliḥan: Their Brother**

The Qur'an's standard formula for a prophet sent to his own people is *akhāhum* — "their brother." It is used of Noah (7:59, 26:106), Hūd (7:65, 26:124), Ṣāliḥ (7:73, 26:142), Shuʿayb (7:85, 26:177), and Lot (26:161). The word establishes three facts at once: the prophet is of the same people, speaks their language, and shares their lineage; his mission is not a foreign imposition; and his people's rejection of him is a rejection of one of their own.

The formula is also a claim about the prophet's *stake*. Ṣāliḥ is not a mercenary teacher or a visiting philosopher. He is a kinsman whose own fate is bound to his people's, and whose warning is therefore an act of loyalty rather than of criticism. The Qur'an makes this explicit in Hūd's words:

> *"I convey unto you the messages of my Lord, and I am unto you a sincere adviser."* (Qur'an 7:68)

**Ani'budū Llāh: The Demand in Three Words**

*"Worship God."* The formula — *uʿbudū Llāh* — is the identical demand in every prophetic mission the Qur'an reports, and the Qur'an says so:

> *"And We sent no messenger before thee but that We revealed unto him: 'There is no god but I, so worship Me.'"* (Qur'an 21:25)

> *"And verily We sent unto every community a messenger: 'Worship God and shun the idol (ṭāghūt).'"* (Qur'an 16:36)

The consistency is the Qur'an's argument for the unity of revelation: the same God, the same demand, in every age and every nation. Al-Naml states the demand here in its shortest form and then reports the effect in the shortest possible terms — and the effect is not obedience but division.

**Fa-Idhā Hum Farīqāni Yakhtaṣimūn: The Immediate Division**

*"And behold, they were two factions, quarreling."* The particle *fa-idhā* — "and then, behold" — is the Qur'an's marker of sudden, unexpected consequence. It is used to introduce an outcome that arrives immediately and surprising:

> *"And [remember] when We said unto the angels: 'Prostrate unto Adam,' and they prostrated, save Iblīs..."* — and: *"And behold (fa-idhā), they are two factions quarreling"* — cf. the same construction at 7:143, 20:18, 36:13

*Farīqāni* is the dual of *farīq*, a group, a party, a faction — a body of people separated from others. The dual form is precise: not several groups, not a general disorder, but exactly two. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the two groups were those who believed in Ṣāliḥ and those who denied him.

*Yakhtaṣimūn* is Form VIII from *kh-ṣ-m*, to dispute, to contend, to argue — the same root as *khaṣm*, an adversary, and *khuṣūmah*, a contention. Form VIII is reciprocal: they contend *with each other*. The Qur'an uses the word of the dispute of the communities at 42:14 (*"and they did not divide until knowledge came to them"*) and of the arguing of the people of the Fire among themselves (40:47).

The verse therefore reports that the arrival of a prophet produced not belief and unbelief sitting side by side, but belief and unbelief in active contention. The two groups are *quarreling* — a present tense denoting an ongoing, reciprocal dispute.

**Why the Verse Emphasizes Division**

Al-Naml's distinctive contribution to the Thamūd narrative is this verse, and its emphasis is deliberate. The other accounts describe the demand, the she-camel, the hamstringing, and the destruction. Al-Naml describes the *civil war* — and then, in verses 48–51, describes a conspiracy of nine men within the community.

The structure of the sūrah's argument requires this emphasis, because al-Naml is about knowledge and about what communities do with it. The verse establishes that the presence of a prophet does not merely add an option to a society; it divides it. There is no middle position available, and the Qur'an says so explicitly:

> *"And when a sūrah is revealed, there are some among them who say: 'Which of you has it increased in faith?' As for those who believe, it increases them in faith, and they rejoice. But as for those in whose hearts is a sickness, it increases them in filth upon their filth, and they die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 9:124–125)

> *"And We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers, but it increases the wrongdoers in nothing but loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

Revelation is not neutral. It clarifies, and clarification divides — because once a question has been posed clearly, every member of the community must answer it, and the answers will not be the same. The two factions of verse 45 are the two factions that any community becomes when a real question is put to it.

**The Two Factions in Every Community**

The verse's application is general and the tradition applies it generally. Every society that receives a prophet, a revelation, a moral claim, or an unwelcome truth divides into two parties, and the parties contend. The contending is not incidental to the process; it is how the truth becomes established in a community — through argument, through example, through persecution, and sometimes through the destruction of one party.

The reader's own position in this structure is not optional. Verse 45 does not describe something that happened to an ancient Arabian tribe; it describes what happens when *ani'budū Llāh* is delivered anywhere, including into a household, a workplace, or a single mind. The two factions form. The contention begins. And the sūrah will now show what the losing faction did about it — which is the subject of verses 46 through 51, and which is among the most precisely observed accounts of conspiracy in the Qur'an.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:46

> **He said, "O my people! Why do you seek to hasten evil before good? Why do you not seek Forgiveness of God, that haply you may be shown mercy?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Answer to the Quarrel**

*Qāla yā qawmi lima tastaʿjilūna bi'l-sayyiʾati qabla'l-ḥasanati law lā tastaghfirūna Llāha laʿallakum turḥamūn* — "He said: 'O my people, why do you hasten the evil before the good? Why do you not seek forgiveness from God, that haply you may be shown mercy?'"

The verse follows immediately upon the report of the two contending factions, and al-Ṭabarī explains the connection: the disbelieving faction had demanded that Ṣāliḥ produce the punishment he threatened, if he was truthful. Their taunt is recorded elsewhere in the Qur'an:

> *"They said: 'O Ṣāliḥ! Thou wast among us a source of hope before this. Dost thou forbid us to worship what our fathers worshipped? Verily we are in troubling doubt concerning that to which thou callest us.'"* (Qur'an 11:62)

> *"They said: 'Bring us the punishment, if thou art among the truthful.'"* — cf. 7:70, 29:29

Ṣāliḥ's reply in verse 46 addresses the taunt directly, and it does so by identifying the taunt itself as the problem.

**Lima Tastaʿjilūna Bi'l-Sayyiʾah: The Verb of Hurrying**

*Tastaʿjilūna* is Form X from *ʿ-j-l*, haste, speed, acceleration. Form X (*istafʿala*) carries the sense of seeking or requesting: *istaʿjala* means to seek haste, to demand that something come quickly, to be impatient for. The Qur'an uses this verb repeatedly of the disbelievers' demand for punishment:

> *"And they would have thee hasten the evil before the good, though examples have passed before them."* (Qur'an 13:6 — cited by the source commentary as the parallel)

> *"And they say: 'O Thou upon whom the Reminder has been sent down, verily thou art possessed. Why dost thou not bring us the angels, if thou art among the truthful?'"* (Qur'an 15:6–7)

> *"And they would have thee hasten the punishment; and verily Hell shall encompass the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 29:53)

> *"Mankind asks thee concerning the Hour. Say: 'The knowledge of it is only with God'"* (Qur'an 33:63)

> *"And they say: 'When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?'"* — the identical taunt appears at verse 71 of this sūrah, and the Qur'an repeats it at 10:48, 21:38, 34:29, 36:48, 67:25

**Bi'l-Sayyiʾati Qabla'l-Ḥasanati: The Inversion Ṣāliḥ Names**

*"The evil before the good."* Al-Ṭabarī explains: the evil is the punishment of God, and the good is His mercy. The people are demanding the punishment in preference to the mercy — asking for the second thing first and refusing the first thing entirely.

Al-Qurṭubī records a further reading, which complements the first: the phrase may also refer to the fact that the Thamūd's own actions were evil and deserved punishment, and that they committed such actions rather than performing good actions, which merit reward. On this reading the inversion is not only in their demand but in their conduct: they produce evil where good is available, and then ask why the consequence has not arrived.

Both readings describe the same disordered sequence, and the disorder is the verse's subject. A rational being seeks the good and avoids the evil; a rational being who has been warned seeks the good *first*, while it is available, and avoids the evil that follows its refusal. Thamūd does the opposite: it refuses the good, performs the evil, and then demands the consequence.

The psychology here is precise and familiar. The demand for punishment is not genuine curiosity about whether the prophet is truthful. It is a *defensive* move: by treating the warning as an absurdity to be tested, the warned convert a moral claim into an empirical bet, and a bet can be lost without being believed. If the punishment does not arrive, the prophet is refuted. If it arrives, it is too late to matter. Either way, no repentance is required today. This is why Ṣāliḥ's answer does not argue about the punishment at all — it moves immediately to the alternative.

**Law Lā Tastaghfirūna Llāh: The Proposal**

*"Why do you not seek forgiveness from God?"* The construction *law lā* — "why not, if only you would" — is a particle of exhortation carrying a note of reproach and of wish: "would that you sought forgiveness." *Tastaghfirūna* is Form X from *gh-f-r*, to cover, to conceal, to protect by covering — the same root as *ghafūr* at verse 11 and *istighfār*, the act of asking for covering.

The proposal is the whole of Ṣāliḥ's answer, and its brevity is its force. He does not propose that they abandon their demand, or that they argue the evidence, or that they wait to see. He proposes a single act — *istighfār* — which is available immediately, costs nothing, requires no proof of his prophethood, and produces a different outcome from the one they are demanding.

The Qur'an presents *istighfār* as the pivotal act in the economy of punishment, and states its efficacy in explicit terms:

> *"And God would not punish them while thou wast among them, nor would God punish them while they sought forgiveness (yastaghfirūn)."* (Qur'an 8:33)

> *"And [Hūd said]: 'O my people, seek forgiveness of your Lord, then turn unto Him; He will send down upon you heaven in abundance, and add strength to your strength. And do not turn away as wrongdoers.'"* (Qur'an 11:52)

> *"And Noah said: 'Verily I called them openly... and I said: "Seek forgiveness of your Lord — verily He is ever Forgiving. He will send down heaven upon you in abundance, and will aid you with wealth and children, and will appoint for you gardens, and will appoint for you rivers."'"* (Qur'an 71:5–12)

> *"And whoever does evil or wrongs himself, then seeks forgiveness of God, will find God Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 4:110)

The three prophetic passages — Noah, Hūd, and Ṣāliḥ — make the same proposal in the same terms: seek forgiveness, and rain, wealth, children, gardens, and rivers follow. *Istighfār* is presented not as a ritual of guilt but as the act that reopens the flow of good. And Thamūd's response to it is recorded at verses 47–49.

**Laʿallakum Turḥamūn: The Purpose and Its Modesty**

*"That haply you may be shown mercy."* The particle *laʿalla* expresses hope and expectation, not guarantee — the same particle Moses used at verse 7 (*laʿallakum taṣṭalūn*, "haply you may warm yourselves"). Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī note that *laʿalla* in the Qur'an, when attributed to God or to a prophet speaking for God, often carries the sense of expectation that is realized, since God's hope-language expresses what He intends to give.

*Turḥamūn* is the passive of Form IV from *r-ḥ-m*, mercy — "that you may be mercied, that mercy may be shown to you." The passive is significant: mercy is not produced by the seeker; it is received. The seeker performs *istighfār*, and the mercy comes from elsewhere.

The modesty of the formulation — "haply, perhaps, that you may" — is a feature of prophetic speech throughout the Qur'an and it has a pastoral purpose. A prophet who promised mercy unconditionally would remove the need for the change of heart that mercy requires; a prophet who threatened punishment unconditionally would remove the possibility of return. The *laʿalla* holds both open, and it places the outcome in a relation rather than in a calculation.

**The Structure of the Verse as a Model of Prophetic Argument**

Verse 46 contains three movements, and their order is instructive.

First, the **diagnosis**: you are inverting the order of good and evil — demanding the consequence and refusing the alternative. The diagnosis is not an accusation of wickedness; it is an identification of a mistake in reasoning, stated as a question (*lima*, "why?"), which invites an answer rather than provoking a defense.

Second, the **proposal**: seek forgiveness. A single, available, costless act, addressed to God rather than to the prophet, requiring no acceptance of the prophet's authority as a precondition.

Third, the **purpose**: that you may be shown mercy. The outcome offered is not intellectual satisfaction but relief — and it is offered with a particle of hope rather than a guarantee, which preserves the seeker's freedom.

This is the Qur'an's standard prophetic method, and it is worth noting how little of it is argumentative. Ṣāliḥ does not prove God's existence, does not demonstrate his own prophethood, does not answer the demand for the punishment. He redirects: you are asking for the wrong thing, here is the right thing to ask for, and here is what follows from asking for it.

The method has direct application in every situation where a person is demanding a consequence rather than seeking a remedy — a patient demanding a diagnosis instead of a treatment, a debtor demanding an explanation instead of a plan, a sinner demanding to know whether he is damned instead of asking to be forgiven. The answer in verse 46 is the correct answer in all such cases: name the inversion, propose the available act, and state the mercy that follows it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:47

> **They said, "We augur ill of you and of those with you." He said, "Your auguring ill is with God. Rather, you are a people being tried."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response of Superstition to the Offer of Mercy**

*Qālū aṭṭayyarnā bika wa bi-man maʿaka qāla ṭāʾirukum ʿinda Llāhi bal antum qawmun tuftanūn* — "They said: 'We have augured ill by you and by those with you.' He said: 'Your augury is with God. Nay, but you are a people being tried.'"

The answer to Ṣāliḥ's proposal of *istighfār* is not a refusal. It is a superstition — and the shift from a religious offer to an omen is one of the Qur'an's most precise observations about how communities evade a moral demand.

**Aṭṭayyarnā: The Verb of Taking Omens**

*Aṭṭayyarnā* is an assimilated form of *taṭayyarnā*, Form V from *ṭ-y-r*, to fly — the same root as *ṭāʾir*, a bird, and *ṭayr*, birds collectively. The practice denoted is *taṭayyur* or *ṭiyarah*: augury, the taking of omens, specifically by the flight or direction of birds.

The source commentary explains the practice: a bird would be released or chased, and if it flew to the right it was a good omen, and if it flew to the left, a bad one. Arabic distinguished *sanīḥ* (a bird passing on the right, favorable) from *bāriḥ* (a bird passing on the left, unfavorable), and the entire practice rested on the belief that events in the natural world carried messages about future fortune.

The Qur'an reports the same response from other peoples, and the source commentary cites the parallels:

> *"But when good came unto them, they said: 'This is for us.' And if evil befell them, they augured ill (yaṭṭayyarū) by Moses and those with him."* (Qur'an 7:131)

> *"They said: 'We augur ill (aṭṭayyarnā) by you. If you desist not, we shall surely stone you, and there shall surely befall you from us a painful punishment.'"* (Qur'an 36:18 — the people of Antioch to the three messengers)

The recurrence is exact: the arrival of a messenger is interpreted as a bad omen. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr note the pattern and explain its logic — the messengers brought warnings, and warnings precede punishments, and a people who associate the warner with the warning come to associate him with the misfortune itself.

**Bika Wa Bi-Man Maʿaka: The Extension to the Believers**

*"By you and by those with you."* The omen extends from the prophet to his followers, and this extension is significant. The two factions of verse 45 are both included in the augury: the prophet and the believing party are jointly identified as sources of bad luck.

This is the social mechanism by which a minority is made responsible for a community's misfortunes, and it operates identically in every age. When a society experiences drought, plague, defeat, or economic decline, the question "why is this happening?" has two possible answers: an account of the society's own conduct, or an account of some group within it that has offended the cosmic order. The second answer is always cheaper, and it is always available — because there is always a minority whose practices differ.

Verse 45 described Thamūd as two factions contending. Verse 47 shows the larger faction resolving the contention by identifying the smaller one as a source of pollution. The move ends the argument without answering it, which is precisely its function.

**Ṭāʾirukum ʿInda Llāh: Ṣāliḥ's Correction**

*"Your augury is with God."* The noun *ṭāʾir* is from the same root — a bird, and by extension an omen, a fortune, a lot. The Qur'an uses the word in this extended sense elsewhere:

> *"And every man's augury (ṭāʾirahu) have We fastened to his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a Book which he shall find wide open."* (Qur'an 17:13)

> *"They said: 'We augur ill by you.' He said: 'Your augury (ṭāʾirukum) is with God.'"* (Qur'an 36:19)

Verse 17:13 is the Qur'an's decisive statement on the matter, and it inverts the superstition completely. A person's *ṭāʾir* — his fortune, his lot, his outcome — is not in a bird. It is fastened to his own neck: it is his deeds, his choices, his record, which will be produced on the Day of Resurrection as a book he will find open.

Al-Qurṭubī explains Ṣāliḥ's answer at verse 47: *"your auguring ill is with God"* means that the things they predicted would happen as a result of ill omens are with God — that is, known only to God, and determined only by Him. Al-Rāzī adds that whatever good or evil they believed would happen to them comes not from any auguring but originates from God.

The correction therefore has two parts. The **locus** of the omen is wrong: it is not in the flight of a bird or in the presence of a prophet; it is with God. And the **mechanism** is wrong: what befalls a people is not caused by an unlucky person among them but by God's decree in response to their own conduct.

**The Prophet's Refutation of Ṭiyarah and Its Legal Consequence**

The Islamic tradition's position on augury is categorical, and the ḥadīth literature states it in terms of unusual directness. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record:

> *"There is no contagion (ʿadwā), no augury (ṭiyarah), no hāmah [a bird of ill omen in pre-Islamic belief], and no ṣafar [an unlucky month or a disease of the belly]. And the believer flees from leprosy as he flees from a lion."* (al-Bukhārī 5757; Muslim 2220, from Abū Hurayrah)

> *"Augury is shirk; augury is shirk; augury is shirk — and there is none of you [who does not feel it], but God removes it by trust (tawakkul)."* (Abū Dāwūd 3910; al-Tirmidhī 1614, graded ṣaḥīḥ, from Ibn Masʿūd)

> *"Whoever is turned back by augury from what he intended has committed shirk." They said: "What is the expiation for that, O Messenger of God?" He said: "To say: 'O God, there is no good but Thy good, and no bird but Thy bird, and there is no god other than Thee.'"* (Aḥmad 2:220, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr)

And the positive alternative:

> *"'I am pleased with my Lord's good fortune (faʾlu lā yuʾaddhinī)' — the Prophet ﷺ was pleased by a good word and by an auspicious name."* (al-Bukhārī 5756; Muslim 2223, in variants: *"I am pleased by good fortune (faʾl)"*; and he was asked *"what is faʾl?"* and said *"a good word"*)

The distinction the Prophet ﷺ drew is precise and it is the same distinction Ṣāliḥ draws in verse 47. *Ṭiyarah* — the bad omen that turns one back from an intended action — is shirk, because it attributes causal efficacy to something other than God and it produces fear of a created thing. *Faʾl* — a good word that pleases — is permitted, because it does not attribute causality to anything; it is simply a pleasant thing heard. The difference is not in the words but in what the hearer believes about them.

**Bal Antum Qawmun Tuftanūn: The Real Situation**

*"Nay, but you are a people being tried."* The particle *bal* strikes out what preceded and substitutes the truth, as at verse 36. The verb *tuftanūn* is the passive of Form IV from *f-t-n*, to test, to try, to put to the proof — the same root as *fitnah*, trial and tribulation, and *fatana*, to assay gold by fire.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that they were being tried precisely by the sending of Ṣāliḥ to them. The trial is not the drought or the difficulty they feared; the trial is the prophet. His presence among them is the test, and their response to him — the augury, the demand for punishment, the conspiracy to come — is the answer the test extracts.

The Qur'an states the principle generally:

> *"And We sent unto no community a prophet but that We seized its people with adversity and hardship, that haply they might humble themselves."* (Qur'an 7:94)

> *"Alif Lām Mīm. Do people suppose that they will be left to say 'We believe' and that they will not be tried? And verily We tried those before them, and God surely knows those who are truthful, and surely knows the liars."* (Qur'an 29:1–3)

The second passage is decisive for understanding verse 47's last clause: trial is not an interruption of faith but its necessary condition, and its function is *disclosure* — to make visible who is truthful and who is lying. Thamūd's augury is the disclosure. They were being tested by a prophet, and under the test they revealed that they believed in birds rather than in God.

**Why Superstition Arrives Exactly When Mercy Is Offered**

There is a final observation about the sequence of verses 46 and 47 that deserves to be stated, because it is one of the Qur'an's most acute psychological insights.

Ṣāliḥ offers forgiveness and mercy. The response is an omen. The connection between the two is not accidental. An offer of mercy requires the recipient to acknowledge fault — *istighfār* is a request for covering, and one only asks for covering if one has something to cover. Acknowledging fault is painful, and it must be done before a specific audience: the prophet who made the offer, and the believing faction standing behind him.

Superstition provides an escape. If the prophet and his followers are themselves the source of misfortune, then there is no fault to acknowledge, no forgiveness to request, and no audience to face. The bad luck is *theirs*, not ours; their presence is the problem, not their message. The omen converts a moral demand into a hygienic problem, and hygienic problems are solved by exclusion rather than by repentance.

This is why the Qur'an's account of superstition is not primarily an account of ignorance about causation. It is an account of a *moral evasion*. Verse 48 will show where the evasion leads: nine men in the city, working corruption, plotting a night assassination. The progression from an omen to a murder plot occupies three verses, and it is not a leap. It is the natural sequence of a community that has decided its problem is a person rather than a practice.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:48

> **And in the city there were nine persons working corruption in the land and not setting matters aright.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Nine Men Named by Number and by Character**

*Wa kāna fī'l-madīnati tisʿatu rahṭin yufsidūna fī'l-arḍ wa lā yuṣliḥūn* — "And there were in the city nine persons who worked corruption in the earth and did not set matters aright."

The verse shifts the narrative from the public dispute of verses 45–47 to a private conspiracy, and it does so with a specificity the Qur'an rarely uses: a number. Nine men. Not "a group," not "the notables," not "some of them" — nine, counted, in a city whose identity is given (*al-madīnah*, the city, a definite noun referring to the principal settlement of Thamūd, identified by the commentators as al-Ḥijr).

**Rahṭ: The Word for a Band of Men**

*Rahṭ* denotes a band, a company, a group of men bound together — typically three to ten, and the classical lexicons specify that a *rahṭ* is a group of men from the same tribe who support one another. The word carries the sense of a faction defined by mutual loyalty rather than by formal office.

The Qur'an uses *rahṭ* in several places, and its usages illuminate this one:

> *"And there were in the city nine persons (rahṭ) who worked corruption in the earth and did not set matters aright."* (Qur'an 27:48)

> *"They said: 'Verily, if the dogs [of Joseph's caravan] should steal...'" — and the use of rahṭ at 18:34: *"And he had fruit, and he said to his companion, arguing with him: 'I am greater than you in wealth and mightier in retinue (ʿadadan).'"*

> *"Nine persons (tisʿatu raḥṭ)"* — the phrase is unique in the Qur'an, and al-Ṭabarī explains that the nine were singled out because they were the ones who worked together to hamstring the she-camel and conspired to assassinate Ṣāliḥ.

**Tisʿatu Rahṭin: The Nine and What They Did**

The identification of the nine with two specific acts — the hamstringing of the she-camel and the plot against Ṣāliḥ — is reported by al-Ṭabarī and adopted by Ibn Kathīr. The she-camel is the sign God gave Thamūd through Ṣāliḥ, described in four places:

> *"This is the she-camel of God, a sign for you. So let her eat in God's earth, and do not harm her, lest a painful punishment seize you."* (Qur'an 7:73)

> *"And We gave unto Thamūd the she-camel, a visible sign, but they wronged her."* (Qur'an 17:59)

> *"Then they called their companion, and he took [a sword] and hamstrung [her]."* (Qur'an 54:29)

> *"And [Ṣāliḥ] said: 'Enjoy yourselves in your homes for three days; that is a promise not to be belied.'"* (Qur'an 11:65)

The sequence is consistent across the accounts: the camel was given as a sign, its watering was regulated between the people and the animal (77:27, 26:155), the people called their most wretched man, he hamstrung her, Ṣāliḥ gave them three days, and the punishment arrived. Verse 49's plot against Ṣāliḥ and his family is placed by the commentators within those three days.

**Yufsidūna Fī'l-Arḍ Wa Lā Yuṣliḥūn: The Pair That Defines Them**

*"Working corruption in the earth and not setting matters aright."* The two verbs are the Qur'an's fundamental pair for social conduct, and the source commentary notes that they are generally contrasted with one another throughout the Book (e.g., 7:56, 7:85, 7:142, 26:152, 29:36).

*Afsada* — Form IV from *f-s-d*, to spoil, corrupt, ruin, put out of order. *Fasād* is the Qur'an's word for the undoing of a settled order: moral corruption, social disorder, economic exploitation, violence, and the destruction of the conditions that make human life possible. The Qur'an uses it of the specific practices of Pharaoh (28:4), of the hypocrites (2:11–12, 2:205), of those who break covenants (13:25), and of the nine here.

*Aṣlaḥa* — Form IV from *ṣ-l-ḥ*, soundness, wholeness, fitness, right ordering. *Iṣlāḥ* is the repair of a thing to its proper condition, and it is the Qur'an's word for reform, reconciliation, and the setting right of affairs:

> *"And if two parties among the believers quarrel, make peace between them."* — and: *"There is no good in much of their secret speech, save [in] him who enjoins charity, or kindness, or reconciliation between people."* (Qur'an 4:114)

> *"And do not work corruption in the earth after its rectification (baʿda iṣlāḥihā)."* (Qur'an 7:56)

> *"So if they reconcile (aṣlaḥā) between them, verily God is Knowing, Wise."* (Qur'an 4:128)

The verse's construction is precise: the nine *worked corruption* — an active verb, a practice they performed — *and did not set matters aright* — a negated verb, a good they never did. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both note that the two halves are not redundant. The first describes what they did; the second describes what they did not do, and the absence is as diagnostic as the presence. There are people who corrupt and also repair, whose lives contain both; there are people who do neither. The nine are described as having *only* the first term: continuous corruption with no counterbalancing rectification anywhere in their conduct.

**The Nine as a Type: The Corruption of the Elite**

Al-Qurṭubī records that these nine were among the notables of the city, and that their evils included, according to some reports, corrupt lending practices and a habit of dishonoring and humiliating people. The reports come from the *isrāʾīliyyāt* and are treated with the reserve discussed at verse 35 — neither confirmed nor denied — but the characterization is consistent with the verse's own vocabulary and with the Qur'an's general presentation of the *malaʾ* who resist prophets.

The structural point the verse makes is that corruption in a community is not evenly distributed. It is concentrated, it is organized, and it is performed by a small number of people with standing. Nine men in a city — a tiny fraction of its population — are identified as the ones who "worked corruption in the earth." The rest of Thamūd is not described as corrupt in the same way; they are described as two factions (v. 45), as hastening evil before good (v. 46), and as taking omens (v. 47). The nine are something else: an organized band whose practice was corruption and whose omission was rectification.

This is one of the Qur'an's most repeated social observations, and it is stated explicitly elsewhere:

> *"And thus We made in every city its greatest wrongdoers (akābira mujrimīhā), that they might plot therein; and they plot not save against themselves, though they are not aware."* (Qur'an 6:123)

> *"And We sent not unto any town a warner but that its wealthy ones (mutrafūhā) said: 'Verily we disbelieve in that with which you have been sent.'"* (Qur'an 34:34)

> *"And Pharaoh called out to his people and said: 'O my people, is not the kingdom of Egypt mine, and these rivers flowing beneath me? Do you not then see?'"* (Qur'an 43:51)

The pattern is: a small group of the powerful, whose interest lies in the existing order, and whose practice is corruption presented as stability. Verse 48 names them, counts them, and describes them in eight words.

**Madīnah: The City as a Setting**

The word *madīnah* — city — is definite: *in the city*, in a specific place. The Qur'an uses the same word of the cities of the destroyed peoples:

> *"And how many a city was mightier in strength than thy city which drove thee out"* (Qur'an 47:13)

> *"And [mention] the city that was secure and at ease"* — cf. 16:112

The definiteness matters. The conspiracy is not a general tendency; it is a plot formed in a particular place by particular men, and the Qur'an's insistence on the particular is what makes verses 51–52 possible — *"behold how their plot fared in the end... And those are their houses, lying desolate."* A general tendency cannot have ruins. Nine men in a city can.

**The Verse's Application: Identifying the Nine**

The practical reach of verse 48 is considerable, and it is not primarily about ancient Arabia. Every community — a city, a company, a mosque, a family, a profession, an online space — contains a small number of people whose practice is corruption and whose omission is rectification. They are usually identifiable, and they are usually few.

The verse supplies the criteria, and they are behavioral rather than reputational. The nine are not described as unpopular, or as disbelievers, or as violent, or as rich. They are described by what they *do* (they work corruption) and by what they *never do* (they do not set matters aright). The second criterion is the one that identifies them reliably, because the first can be disputed: any person accused of corruption will deny it, and the denial will sound sincere. But the absence of rectification is visible over time. A person who, across years, has never repaired a relationship, never reconciled two parties, never defended an absent person, never fixed a broken process, never spoken a word that improved a situation — that person is one of the nine, whatever he says about himself.

The verse also states the number, and the number is a warning against a different error: attributing a community's corruption to the community as a whole. Nine men in a city. The rest were frightened, superstitious, divided, and eventually destroyed — but they were not the nine. A reader who concludes that "everyone is corrupt" has misread the verse, and the misreading produces exactly the paralysis the nine benefit from. The Qur'an's account is more useful and more demanding: find the nine, name what they do, and be one of those who set matters aright.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:49

> **They said, "Swear by God to each other that we shall attack him and his family by night. Then we shall surely say to his heir that we were not present at the destruction of his family and that surely we are truthful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conspiracy in Detail**

*Qālū taqāsamū bi'Llāhi la-nubayyitannahu wa ahlahu thumma la-naqūlanna li-waliyyihī mā shahidnā mahlika ahlihī wa innā la-ṣādiqūn* — "They said: 'Swear to one another by God: we shall certainly attack him and his family by night. Then we shall certainly say to his heir: "We were not present at the destruction of his family" — and we are truthful.'"

This is the Qur'an's most detailed account of a conspiracy, and the detail is not incidental. The verse reports not only the plan but the plan's *cover story*, not only the crime but the perjury arranged in advance. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the nine swore by God to one another that they would kill Ṣāliḥ and provide alibis for one another to his heir — that they wanted an excuse to give to the person upon whom the duty of exacting retribution would fall.

**Taqāsamū Bi'Llāh: The Oath Made Mutual**

*Taqāsamū* is the imperative of Form VI from *q-s-m*, to divide, to allot, to swear — the same root as *qasam*, an oath, and *muqāsamah*, mutual swearing. Form VI denotes reciprocity: *swear to one another.*

The reciprocity is essential to the conspiracy's structure, and the verse's use of it is precise. Nine men planning a murder need nine men committed to the same story. A single oath binds one person; a mutual oath binds the group to each other, so that each man's safety depends on every other man's silence. The conspirators are not merely agreeing to a plan; they are constructing a mechanism of mutual hostage-taking.

They swear *by God* — *bi'Llāh* — and the fact is reported without comment, which makes it more damning. These are men who take omens from birds (v. 47), who demand a prophet produce punishment (v. 46), who reject the offer of forgiveness — and who invoke God's Name as the bond of an assassination plot. Al-Qurṭubī notes the Qur'an's condemnation of this practice elsewhere:

> *"And do not make God a target for your oaths, [as an excuse] against your being righteous and reverent and making peace between people."* (Qur'an 2:224)

> *"And do not sell God's covenant for a small price."* (Qur'an 16:95)

> *"Those who sell God's covenant and their own oaths for a small price — those shall have no share in the Hereafter, and God will not speak unto them, nor look upon them on the Day of Resurrection, nor purify them; and theirs shall be a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 3:77)

**La-Nubayyitannahu Wa Ahlahu: The Night Attack**

*"We shall certainly attack him and his family by night."* The verb *nubayyitanna* is Form II from *b-y-t*, night — the same root as *bayt*, house, and *bāta*, to spend the night. Form II, *bayyata*, means to plan or execute something by night; *tabyīt* in Arabic is specifically the night raid, the attack made under cover of darkness.

The two emphatic particles — the *lām* of oath and the heavy *nūn* — mark the resolve, as at verse 37. The object is doubled: *him and his family*, the prophet and his household. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the plot extended to the family because killing the prophet alone would leave an avenger, and because the destruction of the household would remove the evidence of the crime.

The choice of night is deliberate and the Qur'an notes it elsewhere as characteristic of the conspirator:

> *"They plotted (wa makarū makran) and We plotted (wa makarnā makran), while they were not aware."* — verse 50

> *"Do they feel secure from God's plan? None feels secure from God's plan save a people that are lost."* (Qur'an 7:99)

> *"Or do they plot evil? Then those who disbelieve shall be the ensnared."* — cf. 52:42, 86:15–17, 3:54, 8:30, 13:42

**Thumma La-Naqūlanna Li-Waliyyihī: The Pre-Arranged Lie**

*"Then we shall certainly say to his heir."* *Walī* is from *w-l-y*, nearness, guardianship, authority — the same root as *tawallā* at verse 28 and *walī*, protector, friend, patron. In legal usage, the *walī* of a murdered person is the one entitled to pursue the matter: the heir, the avenger of blood, the nearest male kin, the one upon whom the duty and the right of *qiṣāṣ* (retribution) falls. The concept is legislated in the Qur'an:

> *"O you who believe! Retribution (qiṣāṣ) is prescribed for you in cases of killing..."* and: *"But whoever is forgiven something by his brother [the walī], then let the follow-up be honorable, and let him pay him with goodwill."* (Qur'an 2:178)

> *"And do not kill the soul which God has forbidden, save by right. And whoever is killed wrongfully, We have given his heir (waliyyahu) authority — but let him not exceed the bounds in killing. Verily he is assisted."* (Qur'an 17:33)

The verse at 17:33 is the legal ground of the conspiracy's cover story, and it explains why the nine planned as they did. The *walī* of a murdered prophet has authority to pursue the killers. Nine men cannot defend themselves against a community's law of blood-revenge, so they plan not to avoid the killing but to avoid the *attribution* — and the avoidance requires a story told to the person who has the right to act on the truth.

**Mā Shahidnā Mahlika Ahlihī: The Formulated Denial**

*"We were not present at the destruction of his family."* The verb *shahidnā* is from *sh-h-d*, to be present, to witness, to testify — the root discussed at verse 32 (*ḥattā tashhadūn*, "until you are present"). The conspirators' lie inverts the meaning of the root: they will say they were *not* witnesses, not present, not there.
