*Mahlika ahlihī* — "the destruction of his family" — is from *h-l-k*, perishing, destruction. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī note the grammatical variants in the reading of this word (*mahlika*, *mahlaka*, *muhlika*), which affect whether it denotes the time, the place, or the act of destruction; all convey the same substance.

**Wa Innā La-Ṣādiqūn: The Perjury**

*"And we are truthful."* The final clause of the planned lie is itself an oath — *inna* plus the *lām* of confirmation — the strongest form of assertion in Arabic. The conspirators plan to swear, by God, that they are telling the truth, about a murder they will have committed, to the man entitled to avenge it.

The verse thereby presents a complete structure of religious corruption: a mutual oath by God (v. 49a), a night murder of a prophet and his family (v. 49b), a second oath by God to the victim's heir (v. 49c), and a sworn claim of truthfulness covering the whole (v. 49d). Every element of the conspiracy invokes the Name the conspiracy offends.

This is the Qur'an's definition of *nifāq* at its most extreme, and it is not limited to hypocrites inside a believing community. The nine are not believers pretending; they are disbelievers using the language of belief as an instrument. The Qur'an describes the same phenomenon in general terms:

> *"And of mankind there is he whose speech about the life of this world impresses thee, and he calls God to witness what is in his heart; yet he is the most obstinate of adversaries. And when he turns away, he strives in the earth to work corruption therein, and to destroy the tillage and the stock; and God loves not corruption. And when it is said unto him: 'Revere God,' his pride carries him off to sin. Hell shall suffice him — an evil resting place!"* (Qur'an 2:204–206)

Verse 2:205 uses the identical vocabulary of verse 48 — *li-yufsida fī'l-arḍ*, "to work corruption in the earth" — and the connection is exact. The man described in al-Baqarah and the nine described in al-Naml are the same type, and the type is defined by the same three marks: eloquent religious language, a heart God is called to witness, and a practice of corruption when turned away from an audience.

**The Timing of the Plot**

The source commentary records that according to some, this plot took place three days after the hamstringing of the she-camel (7:77; 11:65; 26:157; 54:29; 91:14) and after Ṣāliḥ had informed them of their impending doom. The three days are specified in the Qur'an:

> *"He said: 'Enjoy yourselves in your homes for three days; that is a promise not to be belied.'"* (Qur'an 11:65)

> *"Then they hamstrung her, and defied the command of their Lord, and said: 'O Ṣāliḥ! Bring us what thou promisest us, if thou art among the messengers.'"* (Qur'an 7:77)

The commentators note various versions of how the plot was foiled and how the nine came to their death, but al-Qurṭubī records that none are definitive. Verse 50–51 give the Qur'an's own account, which is deliberately unspecific about mechanism and specific about outcome: *they devised a plot and We devised a plot, while they were not aware... So behold how their plot fared in the end; truly We destroyed them and their people all together.*

The Qur'an's refusal to narrate the mechanism is characteristic and it is theologically meaningful. The conspirators planned in detail — timing, target, cover story, witnesses, oaths — and the response to their detailed plan is not a counter-plan in detail but a single statement that they were unaware and that they were destroyed. The disproportion is the point: the verse does not need to describe how God's plan worked, because the conspirators never saw it, and neither does the reader need to.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:50

> **And they devised a plot, and We devised a plot, while they were not aware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Verse in the Thamūd Narrative and Its Largest Claim**

*Wa makarū makran wa makarnā makran wa hum lā yashʿurūn* — "And they plotted a plot, and We plotted a plot, while they did not perceive."

Eight words in Arabic, and they state a doctrine about the relation between human planning and divine governance that the Qur'an develops across many passages. The verse is the answer to verse 49's detailed conspiracy, and the answer's brevity is its content.

**Makara Makran: The Cognate Accusative and Its Effect**

The construction *makarū makran* — "they plotted a plot" — uses the cognate accusative (*al-mafʿūl al-muṭlaq*), a verbal noun in the accusative after its own verb. Arabic uses this construction for emphasis, specification, or intensification: *they plotted — a real plotting, plotting indeed.* The same construction appears in the second clause: *wa makarnā makran* — "and We plotted a plot."

The symmetry is exact and deliberate. The same verb, the same cognate accusative, the same subject-position structure, applied first to the nine and then to God. The only differences are the pronouns and the final clause — *while they did not perceive.*

**Makr: The Word and Its Two Uses**

*Makr* is from *m-k-r*, to plan, to scheme, to devise a stratagem, to plot secretly. The word is morally neutral in its root sense: it denotes concealed planning, and concealed planning can be good or evil depending on the planner and the purpose. Arabic uses *makr* of a stratagem in war, of a hidden device, and of a treacherous scheme.

The Qur'an uses the word in both directions and is explicit about the comparison. In related passages, as the source commentary notes, God is described as *best of plotters*:

> *"And they plotted (makarū), and God plotted (wa makara Llāh), and God is the best of plotters (khayru'l-mākirīn)."* (Qur'an 3:54)

> *"And when those who disbelieved plotted against thee to confine thee, or to kill thee, or to drive thee out — they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 8:30)

And elsewhere it is said that plotting belongs to God altogether:

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth and seen how was the end of those before them? They were mightier than them in strength... and they plotted (wa makarū), and unto God belongs plotting altogether (al-makru jamīʿan)."* (Qur'an 13:42)

> *"And do not suppose God heedless of what the wrongdoers do... and they have plotted their plot, and their plot is with God, even if their plot were [enough] to remove the mountains."* (Qur'an 14:46–47)

Almost synonymous with this language, as the source commentary notes, is that of God's "scheming" (*kayd*) against the disbelievers, and the superiority of His scheme is alluded to at 7:99, 52:42, 86:15–17, and 10:21:

> *"Do they feel secure from God's plan (makr)? None feels secure from God's plan save a people that are lost."* (Qur'an 7:99)

> *"Or do they plot a scheme (kaydan)? Then those who disbelieve shall be the ensnared (makīdūn)."* (Qur'an 52:42)

> *"Verily they plot a plot, and I plot a plot; so respite the disbelievers, give them respite a little."* (Qur'an 86:15–17)

**Khayru'l-Mākirīn: What "Best of Plotters" Means**

The phrase *khayru'l-mākirīn* — "the best of plotters" — requires careful handling, and the commentators handled it. The comparison is not between equal competitors, because the Qur'an elsewhere denies that anything is God's equal. Al-Rāzī explains that the phrase is a *mashākalah* — an Arabic rhetorical figure in which a word is used in correspondence with another word for stylistic effect, without implying the same sense: the disbelievers' action is called *makr*, so God's response is called *makr* in the same sentence, while the reality differs entirely.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr give the substantive content: God's *makr* is the fulfillment of His promise to His messengers and the execution of His justice upon their enemies, accomplished by means the enemies do not foresee. It is not treachery, because treachery is the breaking of an undertaking, and God breaks nothing; it is not deception, because He has warned openly through His messengers. It is the *concealment of the means* by which an announced outcome arrives.

The distinction is important and the Qur'an is careful about it. God announces the punishment in advance (v. 46, v. 49's three days, 11:65), warns through the prophet, gives time, and then brings about what He announced by means the warned did not anticipate. The announcement is public; the mechanism is hidden. That combination is justice with wisdom, and it is the opposite of the nine's *makr*, which concealed both the act and the intention and added perjury on top.

**Wa Hum Lā Yashʿurūn: The Clause That Completes the Comparison**

*"And they did not perceive."* The verb *yashʿurūn* is the same one used at verse 18, of Solomon's hosts: *"lest Solomon and his hosts crush you, while they are unaware (wa hum lā yashʿurūn)."* The verbal echo across thirty-two verses is deliberate and it produces a comparison the sūrah intends.

At verse 18, the ant anticipates that great power may crush small creatures *without perceiving it*, and she reasons that a prophet would not do so deliberately. At verse 50, nine men plan a murder *without being perceived by the One they are murdering under*. The first case is an absence of perception that excuses; the second is an absence of perception that condemns.

The root *sh-ʿ-r* — fine perception, sensation, awareness — appears in the Qur'an as a diagnosis of the disbelievers' condition repeatedly:

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'Do not work corruption in the earth,' they say: 'We are only rectifiers.' Verily it is they who are the workers of corruption, but they do not perceive (wa lākin lā yashʿurūn)."* (Qur'an 2:11–12)

> *"And they plotted a plot, and We plotted a plot, while they did not perceive."* (Qur'an 27:50)

> *"They did not perceive [it]"* — cf. 36:29, 12:11

Verse 2:12 is the closest parallel in the Qur'an, and it uses the identical vocabulary of verse 48 (*fasād*) and the identical diagnosis (*lā yashʿurūn*). The Qur'an is stating a single connected proposition across two sūrahs: the people who work corruption do not perceive that they are working corruption, and they do not perceive that they are being perceived.

**The Theology of the Verse: Human Planning Under Divine Governance**

Verse 50 is one of the Qur'an's clearest statements of the relation between human agency and divine decree, and the Muslim theological tradition developed it at length.

The verse affirms both agencies in full. *They plotted a plot* — the plotting is theirs, real, deliberate, detailed (v. 49 gave the plan). *We plotted a plot* — the counter-plotting is God's, real, deliberate, and effective. Neither is reduced to the other. The nine are not puppets whose planning was illusory; God is not a spectator whose governance was contingent.

Al-Ashʿarī, al-Māturīdī, and Ibn Taymiyyah all work within this affirmation, and their differences concern the *manner* of the relation rather than the fact of it. Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation is the one most directly supported by this verse: God creates the servant's capacity to act and the servant's act occurs by the servant's own choice and power, which are themselves created; and God's wisdom arranges the circumstances in which choices occur so that His purposes are fulfilled without any person being compelled. The verse states exactly this arrangement: they plotted, He plotted, and they did not perceive how the two plots related.

The practical consequence for a believer is stated in the Qur'an's own terms:

> *"And whoever trusts in God — He is sufficient for him. Verily God brings His command to pass."* (Qur'an 65:3)

> *"If God helps you, none can overcome you; and if He forsakes you, who can help you after Him? And in God let the believers trust."* (Qur'an 3:160)

The person who knows that verse 50 is true does not need to know how the counter-plot works. He needs only to know that there is one, that it is more real than the plot against him, and that its agents are unaware of it. That knowledge is what makes it possible to face a conspiracy — nine men, a night, an oath, and a prepared lie — without despair.

**The Verse's Application**

The application requires little extension. Every person who has done right in public has been plotted against in private. Every institution that has attempted reform has had its nine. The plot always has the same four elements: a mutual oath, a concealed timing, a target including the target's family or associates, and a prepared story for whoever has the right to act on the truth.

Verse 50 supplies the believer's response, and the response is not a counter-strategy. It is a statement of fact: *they plotted, and We plotted, and they did not perceive.* The believer's task is not to out-plan the planners — a task at which they will usually win, since they have fewer constraints. The believer's task is to remain inside the second clause: to be one of those whose affairs are governed by the plot he cannot see, which requires only that he not join the plot he can.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:51

> **So behold how their plot fared in the end; truly We destroyed them and their people all together.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reader Addressed Directly**

*Fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu makrihim annā dammarnāhum wa qawmahum ajmaʿīn* — "Then behold how was the end of their plotting! Verily We destroyed them and their people, all together."

The verse opens with the imperative *fa-nẓur* — "then look, behold, consider" — the fifth use of the root *n-ẓar* in this sūrah (vv. 27, 28, 33, 35, 41), and the first use in the imperative addressed to the reader rather than to a character in the narrative. Solomon looked to test a bird. The council looked to receive a command. The queen looked to see what her envoys returned. Now the reader is told to look at the outcome of a conspiracy.

**Kayfa Kāna ʿĀqibatu Makrihim: The Question Put to History**

*"How was the end of their plotting?"* The construction is an indirect question introduced by *kayfa* — how, in what manner — and it is one of the Qur'an's most characteristic formulas. The source commentary notes that asking how people fared in the end is a common refrain, an invitation to meditate upon the ultimate downfall of previous peoples who were wicked:

> *"And We sent not unto any town a prophet but that We seized its people with adversity and hardship... Then We replaced the evil with the good until they increased and said: 'Adversity and ease befell our fathers too' — so We seized them suddenly, while they did not perceive."* (Qur'an 7:94–95)

> *"Say: 'Journey in the earth, then see how was the end of the deniers.'"* (Qur'an 6:11)

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth and seen how was the end of those before them? They were more numerous than them, and mightier in strength, and [in] the monuments they left in the earth; but what they used to earn availed them not."* (Qur'an 40:82)

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth, that they might have hearts by which to understand, or ears by which to hear? Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is hearts within breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

> *"And how many a sign in the heavens and the earth they pass by, turning away from it."* (Qur'an 12:105)

The parallel instances at 12:109, 30:9, 30:42, 35:44, 40:21, 40:82, and 47:10 all use the same formula, and the formula's function is consistent: it converts a historical report into an instruction. The Qur'an does not ask the reader to accept its account of the destroyed peoples; it asks the reader to go and examine the evidence.

**ʿĀqibah: The Word for the End**

*ʿĀqibah* is from *ʿ-q-b*, heel, to follow at the heels — the *ʿāqibah* is what follows a thing at its heels, its consequence, its final outcome. The word appears at verse 14 of this sūrah in the identical construction (*kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu'l-mufsidīn*, "how was the end of the workers of corruption"), and the two verses are deliberately parallel: Pharaoh's courtiers and Thamūd's nine are described with the same vocabulary and given the same ending.

The Qur'an's use of *ʿāqibah* states a doctrine: every action has a heel that follows it. The doctrine is not karma — the Qur'an is explicit that God may forgive, may delay, may replace evil with good — but it is the assertion that the moral order of the world produces consequences, and that the consequences are visible in history for anyone who looks.

**Annā Dammarnāhum: The Verb of Total Destruction**

*"Verily We destroyed them."* *Dammarnā* is Form II from *d-m-r*, to destroy, to annihilate, to wipe out — the intensive form denoting complete destruction. The Qur'an uses the verb of the destroyed peoples:

> *"And how many a generation have We destroyed (wa kam ahlaknā) before them"* — and the specific verb *dammara* at 47:13: *"And We destroyed it utterly (fa-dammarnāhā)"*; at 25:39: *"and We destroyed them with an utter destruction (fa-dammarnāhum tadmīran)"*

> *"And We destroyed (wa dammarnā) what Pharaoh and his people had made, and what they had built."* (Qur'an 7:137)

The cognate accusative at 25:39 — *tadmīran* — intensifies the verb as verse 50's *makran* intensified *makarū*. The Qur'an's vocabulary of destruction is emphatic, and the emphasis is proportional to the emphatic quality of the plotting it answers.

**Wa Qawmahum Ajmaʿīn: The Scope of the Destruction**

*"And their people, all together."* The word *ajmaʿīn* is the accusative plural of *ajmaʿ*, the totality, the whole — used as an emphatic confirmation of a preceding plural, meaning "all of them, every one, collectively." Arabic uses the construction for emphasis after a noun: *qawmahum ajmaʿīn*, "their people, all of them."

The scope raises a question the commentators address: were the nine destroyed alone, or their whole community? Al-Ṭabarī explains that the destruction of "their people all together" refers to the destruction of Thamūd generally, which the Qur'an describes in several modes:

> *"And as for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance, so the blast of the humiliating punishment (ṣāʿiqat al-ʿadhābi'l-hūn) seized them for what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 41:17)

> *"Verily We sent upon them a single cry (ṣayḥatan wāḥidah), and they became like the dry twigs of a fence-builder."* (Qur'an 54:31)

> *"And the earthquake seized them, and they became, within their homes, fallen upon their knees."* (Qur'an 7:78)

> *"And the blast seized those who wronged, and they became, within their homes, motionless."* (Qur'an 11:67)

The accounts describe the same event from different aspects — a cry, a blast, an earthquake — and the tradition reconciles them as successive moments of a single punishment. The destruction of the nine specifically is reported in the commentaries in various versions, and al-Qurṭubī records that none is definitive; the Qur'an's own statement is that they were destroyed with their people.

The theological point of *ajmaʿīn* is that the conspiracy did not save them and did not distinguish them. Nine men who bound themselves together by a mutual oath, who planned the timing and the target and the cover story, who made their fates dependent on each other's silence — were destroyed together with the community whose corruption they had organized. The unity they constructed for the plot became the unity of their destruction.

**The Verse's Refrain and Its Function in Al-Naml**

*Fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu makrihim* is the second of the sūrah's two uses of this refrain (the first at verse 14). The placement of the two is significant: the first closes the Mosaic narrative, the second closes the Thamūd narrative. Both use the imperative of looking, both use the word *ʿāqibah*, and both direct the reader's attention to the outcome rather than to the argument.

The refrain's function within the sūrah's overall design is now clear. Al-Naml presents case studies — Pharaoh, Thamūd, Lot — and closes each with an invitation to look at the end. The invitation is not rhetorical; it is the sūrah's method of proof. Verse 69 will generalize it (*"Journey upon the earth and behold how the guilty fared in the end!"*), and verse 93 will announce it as God's own procedure (*"He will show you His signs, and you will know them"*).

The reader is thus being trained, across the whole sūrah, in a single habit: when a moral claim is contested, look at the outcomes. Not the arguments — the outcomes. The nine men of Thamūd had arguments (v. 47: you are a bad omen; v. 46: bring the punishment if you are truthful). Their arguments are not answered in the Qur'an with counter-arguments. They are answered with a ruin, and the reader is told to go and see it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:52

> **And those are their houses, lying desolate for their having done wrong. Surely in this is a sign for a people who know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ruins as Evidence, Pointed At**

*Fa-tilka buyūtuhum khāwiatan bi-mā ẓalamū inna fī dhālika la-āyatan li-qawmin yaʿlamūn* — "So those are their houses, fallen desolate, because of their wrongdoing. Verily in that is a sign for a people who know."

The verse moves from the event to its remains, and it points at them. *Fa-tilka* — "so those" — the distant feminine demonstrative, the same one used at verse 1 (*tilka āyātu'l-Qurʾān*). Al-Zamakhsharī notes the effect of the distant demonstrative in such contexts: it indicates something present to the mind's eye, established, and remote in rank or in time. Here it indicates the ruins themselves, which the Qur'an's first hearers could visit.

**Buyūtuhum Khāwiyatan: The Houses and Their State**

*Buyūt* is the plural of *bayt*, house — from *b-y-t*, the root of night and of spending the night (discussed at verse 49, where the conspirators planned *nubayyitannahu*, to attack by night). A *bayt* is a dwelling, the place where a household passes the night, and the Qur'an uses the word both of ordinary houses and of the House of God (*al-bayt*, the Kaʿbah).

The verbal echo between verse 49 and verse 52 is exact and terrible. They plotted to attack Ṣāliḥ and his family *by night* — *la-nubayyitannahu wa ahlahu* — in their houses. The result is that their own houses stand empty. The word for night and the word for house share a root, and the Qur'an uses the root at both ends of the story: the night of the conspiracy and the houses left by it.

*Khāwiyatan* is the active participle of *kh-w-y*, to be empty, hollow, void, collapsed, roofless. The Qur'an uses the word of destroyed settlements:

> *"And how many a city have We destroyed while it was entrenched in wrongdoing, so it lies fallen in upon its roofs (fa-hiya khāwiyatun ʿalā ʿurūshihā), and [how many] a deserted well and a lofty palace!"* (Qur'an 22:45)

> *"So those are their houses, fallen desolate (khāwiyah) because of their wrongdoing."* (Qur'an 27:52)

> *"And it was said: 'O earth, swallow thy water; and O sky, withhold' — and the water subsided, and the matter was accomplished, and [the Ark] settled upon al-Jūdī, and it was said: 'Away with the wrongdoing people!'"* — and the parallel description of ruins at 16:26: *"Those before them plotted; so God pulled down their building from its foundations, and the roof fell upon them from above them, and the punishment came unto them from where they did not perceive."*

Verse 16:26 is a precise counterpart to verse 49: they plotted by night in their houses; the roof fell on them from above; the punishment came from where they did not perceive (*min ḥaythu lā yashʿurūn* — the same root as verse 50's *lā yashʿurūn*).

Al-Ṭabarī explains *khāwiyah* as collapsed and empty — the roofs fallen in, the walls standing or toppled, the interiors void of inhabitants. Al-Qurṭubī records a further interpretation, from Sufi sources, that beyond the outward sense of their now-empty dwellings, the phrase indicates that their *hearts* were hardened through their disobedience — the emptiness of the houses corresponding to the emptiness of the chests.

**Bi-Mā Ẓalamū: The Cause Stated**

*"Because of their wrongdoing."* The construction *bi-mā* — "by what, because of what" — attaches the cause explicitly, and the verb is *ẓalamū*, the same root discussed at verses 14 and 44: *ẓ-l-m*, to place a thing where it does not belong.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that the wrong they did was their idolatry and their denial of their prophet. The verse thereby supplies the causal link the reader needs: the ruins are not the result of an earthquake, an invasion, or a change in climate. They are the result of *ẓulm*. The Qur'an states the same principle in general terms:

> *"And We destroyed not a city but that it had warners, as a reminder; and We were never unjust."* (Qur'an 26:208–209)

> *"So each [of them] We seized for his sin: of them was he upon whom We sent a hurricane, and of them was he whom the Cry seized, and of them was he whom We caused the earth to swallow, and of them was he whom We drowned. And God would not wrong them, but they were wronging themselves."* (Qur'an 29:40)

> *"That is because God is not such as to change a favor He has conferred upon a people until they change what is in themselves."* (Qur'an 8:53)

Verse 29:40 enumerates the modes of destruction and states the principle that governs them all: God did not wrong them; they wronged themselves. And verse 52's *bi-mā ẓalamū* is the same principle applied to a specific set of houses.

**Inna Fī Dhālika La-Āyatan: The Sign Claimed**

*"Verily in that is a sign."* The clause carries *inna* and the emphatic *lām*, and it uses the word *āyah* — sign, proof, wonder — the same word verse 1 used of the Qur'an's own units (*tilka āyātu'l-Qurʾān*). The connection is deliberate: the ruins are *āyāt* in the same sense that the verses are *āyāt*. Both are signs, both point beyond themselves, and both require reading.

The Qur'an makes this equivalence explicit in its repeated command to travel and look:

> *"Say: 'Journey in the earth and see how He originated creation.'"* (Qur'an 29:20)

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth, that they might have hearts by which to understand, or ears by which to hear? Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is hearts within breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

> *"And [We made them] an example (mathal) for what is after them, and a lesson (muttaqa) for those who revere."* — cf. 43:56

**Li-Qawmin Yaʿlamūn: The Sign's Audience**

*"For a people who know."* The qualification limits the sign's audience, and the limitation is epistemic rather than social. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note that the Qur'an uses three such qualifications for its signs, in ascending order: *li-qawmin yaʿlamūn* (for a people who know), *li-qawmin yatafakkarūn* (for a people who reflect), and *li-qawmin yaʿqilūn* (for a people who use their reason):

> *"And among His signs is the creation of the heavens and the earth and the diversity of your tongues and your colors. Verily in that are signs for those who know (li'l-ʿālimīn)."* (Qur'an 30:22)

> *"Verily in the heavens and the earth are signs for the believers... and in the alternation of night and day, and the provision God sends down from the sky whereby He revives the earth after its death, and the turning of the winds — signs for a people who use their reason (li-qawmin yaʿqilūn)."* (Qur'an 45:3–5)

> *"And He has subjected unto you the night and the day, and the sun and the moon... Verily in that are signs for a people who reflect (yatafakkarūn)."* — cf. 16:12, 30:21, 13:3

The ruins of Thamūd are addressed to the first class: people who *know*. The knowledge required is not expertise but a specific competence — the ability to read an effect back to its cause. A person who looks at collapsed houses and sees only geology has not received the sign. A person who looks and asks *what did the inhabitants of these houses do?* has.

**Why the Verse Points at Physical Ruins**

The Qur'an's insistence on physical evidence is one of its most distinctive features, and verse 52 is a clear instance. The claim being made — that God destroys communities for *ẓulm* — is a claim about history and morality, and it is being evidenced by *architecture*.

The method is empirical in the strict sense: an observable, revisitable, datable set of objects is offered in support of a general proposition. The reader is not asked to accept a report; he is asked to inspect a site. The site is identified (Thamūd's city, al-Ḥijr), its state is described (houses fallen in, empty), and its cause is asserted (their wrongdoing).

The Prophet ﷺ's own treatment of al-Ḥijr, recorded in the two Ṣaḥīḥs and cited at verse 45, confirms that the site was treated as real and visitable: he ordered the Companions not to enter the dwellings of the destroyed except while weeping, and to pour out the food cooked with the local water. The instruction presupposes that the ruins are there, that they are the ruins of the people the Qur'an describes, and that a believer standing among them is standing in evidence.

**The Verse's Application to Every Reader With a Ruin Nearby**

Every civilization has its *buyūt khāwiyah*. Every reader lives within reach of something that was once inhabited and now is not: a village emptied by migration, a factory closed, a church converted, a house demolished, a district that was prosperous and is not. The Qur'an's instruction is not to mourn these things as loss but to read them as signs — and reading them requires asking the verse's question: *bi-mā ẓalamū?* What was placed where it did not belong?

The question is difficult to ask honestly about one's own civilization, because the answer is usually available and usually unwelcome. Verse 48 supplied the vocabulary for it: corruption practiced and rectification omitted. A ruin is what remains when a community's nine have prevailed long enough. The sign is offered *li-qawmin yaʿlamūn* — to people who know — and the knowing consists precisely in being willing to ask, of the houses one passes every day, what they are evidence of.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:53

> **We saved those who believed and were reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Verse in the Thamūd Narrative and Its Completeness**

*Wa anjaynā'lladhīna āmanū wa kānū yattaqūn* — "And We saved those who believed and were reverent."

Six words in Arabic close the case of Thamūd. The destruction has been described (v. 51), the ruins pointed at (v. 52), and now the salvation is stated — and it is stated with two conditions, neither of which is sufficient alone.

**Anjaynā: The Verb of Deliverance**

*Anjaynā* is Form IV from *n-j-w*, to be saved, to escape, to be delivered from danger — the same root as *najāt*, salvation, *najāh*, success, and *munjūn*, those who are saved. The Qur'an uses the verb constantly of God's deliverance of His prophets and their followers:

> *"And We saved (fa-anjaynāhu) him and those with him by a mercy from Us, and We cut off the root of those who denied Our signs, and they were not believers."* (Qur'an 7:72 — of Noah)

> *"And when Our command came, We saved Hūd and those who believed with him, by a mercy from Us, and We saved them from a harsh punishment."* (Qur'an 11:58)

> *"And when Our command came, We saved Ṣāliḥ and those who believed with him, by a mercy from Us, and from the disgrace of that Day. Verily thy Lord, He is the Strong, the Mighty."* (Qur'an 11:66)

> *"And We saved those who believed and were reverent."* (Qur'an 27:53)

Verse 11:66 is the parallel account of the same event, and its wording adds the elements our verse compresses: the salvation came *by a mercy from Us* (bi-raḥmatin minnā) and it saved them *from the disgrace of that Day* (min khizyi yawmi'idh). Al-Naml's version gives the two conditions on the human side and leaves the divine cause implicit.

**Alladhīna Āmanū: The First Condition**

*"Those who believed."* *Āmanū* is Form IV from *ʾ-m-n*, safety, security, trust — the same root as *amīn* (trustworthy) at verse 39 and *īmān*, faith. Faith in the Qur'an's technical sense is not mere assent; it is the state of a person who has entrusted himself to God, and the root's meaning of safety governs the derivative: the believer is one who has placed himself where he is safe.

Verse 14 of this sūrah established the counter-case with devastating precision: Pharaoh's people rejected the signs *though their souls were convinced of them*. Assent without submission is not *īmān*. Thamūd's believing party is therefore distinguished not by having been persuaded — the she-camel persuaded everyone who saw it — but by having *entrusted* themselves to what they were persuaded of.

**Wa Kānū Yattaqūn: The Second Condition**

*"And were reverent."* The construction *kānū* plus the imperfect *yattaqūn* denotes a continuing, settled state: they were, and had been, people who practiced *taqwā*. The verb is Form VIII from *w-q-y*, to guard, to shield, to protect — *taqwā* is the act of placing a shield between oneself and harm, and in religion, the continuous guarding of the soul against what would rupture its relation to its Lord.

The term was developed at length in the commentary on 2:2, and its analogy — the experienced driver on a dangerous mountain road at night, not paralyzed and not reckless, but constantly *aware* — applies exactly here. Thamūd's believers lived in a community of two contending factions (v. 45), under a conspiracy of nine organized men (vv. 48–49), after a prophet had announced a three-day deadline (11:65). To be *muttaqī* in those conditions was not a feeling; it was a practice maintained under pressure, day after day, while the majority took omens and demanded punishment.

**Why Both Conditions Are Stated**

The pairing *āmanū wa kānū yattaqūn* is one of the Qur'an's most frequent formulas:

> *"Verily, those who believe and work righteousness — for them is a reward unceasing."* — and: *"Verily, for the reverent (al-muttaqīn) is a place of triumph."* (Qur'an 78:31)

> *"And We saved those who believed and were reverent."* (Qur'an 27:53; cf. 41:18)

> *"Then We saved Our messengers and those who believed. So it is incumbent upon Us to save the believers."* (Qur'an 10:103)

> *"Verily those who said 'Our Lord is God' and then were upright (istiqāmū) — the angels descend upon them: 'Fear not, nor grieve, and rejoice in the Garden that you were promised.'"* (Qur'an 41:30)

The two terms are not redundant, and the commentators explain their relation. Faith is the root; *taqwā* is the practice that protects the root. A person who believes and does not guard will, under pressure, act against his belief; a person who guards without believing is practicing discipline with no object. The Qur'an requires both, and verse 53 states both, because the situation it describes required both: belief alone would not have kept a Thamūdi believer from flinching when the nine came for his household, and caution alone would not have told him what to be cautious about.

**The Saved: Where They Went**

The source commentary records the reports about the saved community's destination. Some commentators say that those who were saved with Ṣāliḥ settled in Ramallah in Palestine (al-Ṭabarī records this), while others say the saved, numbering some four thousand, went to Ḥaḍramawt, where Ṣāliḥ died. Al-Qurṭubī notes the etymology offered for Ḥaḍramawt — that the place is so called because it is where Ṣāliḥ died, since the word can be analyzed as "death has come" (*ḥaḍara mawt*) — and he adds that this is one of several legendary etymologies for the place-name.

The scholarly treatment of these reports follows the rule established at verse 35: they are *isrāʾīliyyāt* and historical traditions, neither confirmed nor contradicted by revelation, and they add color without adding doctrine. The Qur'an's own statement is complete without them: *We saved those who believed and were reverent.* Where they went is not the point; that they were saved, and on what conditions, is.

**The Structural Function of the Verse in Al-Naml**

Verse 53 closes the third of the sūrah's four narrative case studies, and its brevity is structurally meaningful. The sūrah has devoted nine verses to Thamūd (45–53), and it distributes them thus: one verse for the mission and the division (45), one for the prophet's offer (46), one for the superstition (47), one for the nine (48), one for the conspiracy (49), one for the counter-plot (50), one for the destruction (51), one for the ruins (52), and one for the salvation (53).

The distribution tells the reader what the story is about. The largest share goes not to the miracle — the she-camel is not described in al-Naml's account at all — but to the *social process*: division, offer, evasion, organization, conspiracy, and outcome. Al-Naml's Thamūd is a case study in how a community handles a prophet, and verse 53 is its conclusion: a small number believed and guarded, and they were saved.

The same structure governs the Lot narrative that follows (vv. 54–58) and the Solomonic narrative that preceded (vv. 15–44). In each case the sūrah reports the demand, the responses, and the separation — and in each case the separation is the point. Verse 3 divided the believers by certainty; verse 4 divided the disbelievers by adorned wandering; verse 45 divided Thamūd into two factions; and verse 53 states which faction was saved. The sūrah is building toward its eschatological conclusion, where the same division will be final and visible to everyone: verses 83–90.

**The Verse's Application**

The application is immediate and it is not comfortable. Verse 53 says that a community was destroyed and that the saved were those who believed and guarded — and the community that was destroyed included people who had heard a prophet, seen a sign, and known exactly what was coming. They were not ignorant. They were not deceived. They were warned three days in advance, in explicit terms, by a man they had known all their lives.

The reader's position is analogous and the analogy is the reason the verse exists. The warning has been given; the ruins are available for inspection; the conditions of salvation are stated in six words. What remains is the practice — *kānū yattaqūn*, they were guarding — which is not a single decision but a continuing state maintained under social pressure, in a city with nine men in it, until the roof comes down.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:54

> **And [We sent] Lot, when he said to his people, "Do you commit indecency, though you see?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Case Study, Compressed**

*Wa Lūṭan idh qāla li-qawmihī a-taʾtūna'l-fāḥishata wa antum tubṣirūn* — "And Lot — [remember] when he said to his people: 'Do you commit indecency while you see?'"

With verse 54 the sūrah turns to its fourth narrative, and the compression is marked. The Lot story occupies five verses here (54–58) against nine for Thamūd and thirty for Solomon. The Qur'an narrates Lot at greater length elsewhere — 7:80–84, 11:77–83, 15:57–77, 21:74–75, 26:160–175, 29:28–35, 37:133–138, 54:33–39 — and the source commentary directs the reader to 11:77–83 for the full account. Al-Naml's version is not a retelling but an extract: it takes from the story the single exchange that carries the sūrah's argument.

The extract is chosen with precision. Of everything in Lot's narrative — the angels' arrival, the guests, the mob at the door, the offer of the daughters, the departure at night, the overturned cities, the wife — al-Naml selects three moments: the confrontation (vv. 54–55), the people's reply (v. 56), and the outcome (vv. 57–58). Everything else is left to the parallel sūrahs. The selection tells the reader what al-Naml wants from Lot: a case study in what a community does when a moral demand is put to it clearly, and the demand is answered with expulsion.

**Wa Lūṭan: The Accusative of Continuation**

The verse begins with *wa Lūṭan* — "and Lot" — in the accusative, without a verb. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain the construction: the verb is supplied from what preceded, either *"and [We sent] Lot"* — continuing the series begun at verse 45, *"We sent to Thamūd their brother Ṣāliḥ"* — or *"and [remember] Lot, when..."* Both readings are attested and both produce the same sense: Lot is the next in the sequence of messengers whose cases the sūrah is presenting.

The supplied verb matters for the sūrah's structure. Al-Naml is constructing a series: Moses (v. 7), Solomon (v. 15), Ṣāliḥ (v. 45), Lot (v. 54). Each is introduced with the language of sending, and each case ends with a separation between those who responded and those who did not. The series is not chronological — Solomon is later than Moses but earlier in the sūrah's arrangement than Ṣāliḥ and Lot — and the arrangement is thematic: the sūrah moves from the tyrant who was certain and refused, to the sovereign who was tested and submitted, to the community that conspired, to the community that expelled its pure.

**Lot's Position in the Qur'an's Account**

Lot (*Lūṭ*) is the nephew of Abraham in the Qur'an's account, and he is described as believing in Abraham and migrating with him:

> *"And Lot believed him, and [Abraham] said: 'Verily I am an emigrant unto my Lord. Verily He is the Mighty, the Wise.'"* (Qur'an 29:26)

> *"And We gave him [Abraham] Isaac and Jacob as a gift, and each We made among the righteous."* — and: *"And Lot — We gave him judgment and knowledge, and We saved him from the town that practiced the abominations. Verily they were an evil people, dissolute."* (Qur'an 21:74)

The Qur'an gives Lot the same title it gives every prophet — *rasūl*, messenger — and states his mission in the standard formula:

> *"When their brother Lot said unto them: 'Will you not revere [God]? Verily I am unto you a trustworthy messenger.'"* (Qur'an 26:161–162)

Verse 21:74 supplies the evaluation of his people that al-Naml will develop: they were *qawma sawʾ fāsiqīn* — an evil people, dissolute. *Fāsiq* is the word discussed at verse 12 — one who has exited obedience, who has come out of the boundary. The Qur'an applies it to Lot's people as a settled description, and verse 55 will confirm it with a second term: *an ignorant people*.

**A-Taʾtūna'l-Fāḥishah: The Question and Its Word**

*"Do you commit indecency?"* The interrogative *a-* opens a question of reproach — not a request for information but a demand for acknowledgment. Lot is not asking whether they do it; he knows they do. He is asking how they can.

*Fāḥishah* is from *f-ḥ-sh*, excess, obscenity, that which exceeds all bounds of what is acceptable. The word denotes an act so gross that it exceeds the ordinary categories of wrongdoing, and the Qur'an uses it for the most severe sexual transgressions and for indecency generally:

> *"And do not approach unlawful sexual intercourse (al-zinā). Verily it is a fāḥishah and an evil way."* (Qur'an 17:32)

> *"Say: 'My Lord has forbidden the fawāḥish, what is apparent of them and what is concealed, and sin, and unjustified aggression...'"* (Qur'an 7:33)

> *"And Satan promises you poverty and commands you to indecency (bi'l-faḥshāʾ), while God promises you forgiveness from Him and bounty."* (Qur'an 2:268)

The source commentary records the exegetical determination of what *al-fāḥishah* denotes here. As discussed at 26:165–166, the indecency is understood to refer to anal intercourse, especially by men with men, but also according to some with women (al-Ṭabarī records this). The determination is based on the following verse, which specifies the act, and on the parallel accounts — particularly 7:80–81 and 29:28–29, which are explicit:

> *"And Lot, when he said unto his people: 'Do you commit such indecency as no one among the worlds has committed before you? Verily you approach men with desire instead of women. Nay, but you are an excessing people.'"* (Qur'an 7:80–81)

> *"And Lot, when he said unto his people: 'Verily you commit such indecency as no one among the worlds has committed before you. Verily you approach men and cut off the road and commit indecency in your gatherings.'"* (Qur'an 29:28–29)

**Wa Antum Tubṣirūn: "While You See"**

*"And you see."* The clause is a circumstantial qualifier (*ḥāl*) and it is the distinctive contribution of al-Naml's version. The other accounts say "as no one among the worlds has committed before you" (7:80) or add "and you commit indecency in your gatherings" (29:29). Al-Naml says: *while you see.*

Al-Rāzī draws out the meaning, as the source commentary records: part of their sin was that they were open about their illicit activity and did not seek to keep it from view. *Tubṣirūn* is from *b-ṣ-r*, sight and insight — the same root as *mubṣirah* at verse 13, where the signs came *clear and self-evident*, and as *baṣīrah*, insight, self-awareness.

The word therefore carries both senses, and the commentators accept both. On the first, the clause means: you commit this *publicly*, in the open, without concealment — and the openness is itself an aggravation, because concealment at least preserves the community's standard and its shame. On the second, it means: you do it *knowingly*, with your eyes open, seeing what you are doing — and the knowledge is the aggravation, because it removes the excuse of ignorance.

Both readings are supported by the Qur'an's usage of the root, and both are supported by the verse that follows, which diagnoses them as *an ignorant people* — meaning, in the commentators' explanation, ignorant of the consequence rather than unaware of the act. They saw clearly and knew nothing.

The pairing of *tubṣirūn* here with *faḥīshah* is one of the Qur'an's sharpest juxtapositions, and it recalls verse 13 in reverse. There, the signs came *mubṣirah* — luminous, making one see — and the people called them sorcery. Here, the people are *tubṣirūn* — seeing — and what they see does not stop them. Sight in the Qur'an is not a guarantee of perception, and verse 54 is one of the sūrah's demonstrations.

**The Argumentative Structure of Lot's Question**

The verse's two clauses form a complete argument, and its structure deserves to be set out.

The first clause — *do you commit indecency?* — asserts the moral classification of the act. It is called *fāḥishah*, which places it in the Qur'an's category of acts that exceed all bounds: not a preference, not a variation, not a private matter, but an excess. The classification is not argued for in this verse; it is stated, on the authority of the messenger, and the parallel accounts supply the reasoning (the act is unprecedented among the worlds, 7:80; it cuts off the road, 29:29; it is against the wisdom of God's creation, as al-Rāzī notes).

The second clause — *while you see* — removes the available defense. Whatever else the people might plead, they cannot plead ignorance, confusion, or privacy. They do it openly, with their eyes open, in their gatherings. The defense is closed before the argument begins.

This is the Qur'an's standard prophetic method, seen at verse 46 (Ṣāliḥ's *why do you hasten evil before good?*) and at verse 14 (Pharaoh's people rejecting what their souls were convinced of). The prophet does not primarily argue; he *diagnoses*. He names the act, identifies the defense the actors are relying on, and removes it. What remains is the actors' own will — which is why the response in verse 56 is not a counter-argument but an expulsion order.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:55

> **Do you really come with desire unto men instead of women? Nay, but you are an ignorant people!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Specification**

*A-innakum la-taʾtūna'l-rijāla shahwatan min dūni'l-nisāʾ bal antum qawmun tajhalūn* — "Do you really approach men with desire instead of women? Nay, but you are an ignorant people!"

Verse 54 named the act as *fāḥishah*; verse 55 specifies it. The specification is given with two emphatic particles — *a-innakum la-taʾtūna* — the interrogative *a-* combined with *inna* and the *lām* of confirmation, producing in Arabic a question of astonished reproach: "is it really the case that you...?" The construction conveys not doubt but incredulity at something established.

**Taʾtūna'l-Rijāla Shahwatan: The Act Described**

*"You approach men with desire."* The verb *taʾtūna* is from *ʾ-t-y*, to come, to approach, to go to — used in the Qur'an of approaching a person sexually (as at 2:222–223) and of committing an act (as at 7:81). The object is *al-rijāl* — men, adult males — and the qualifier *shahwatan* is the accusative of cause or manner: *out of desire, in desire, driven by desire.*

*Shahwah* is from *sh-h-y*, appetite, longing, desire — the same root as *ishtahā*, to crave, and *shahawāt*, desires. The Qur'an uses the word of legitimate desire as well:

> *"Adorned for mankind is the love of desires (shahawāt) — of women and sons and heaped-up hoards of gold and silver, and branded horses, and cattle and tillage. That is the enjoyment of the life of this world; and with God is the beautiful place of return."* (Qur'an 3:14)

> *"And those who guard their private parts, save from their wives or what their right hands possess — for then they are not blameworthy; but whoever seeks beyond that, those are the transgressors."* (Qur'an 23:5–7; 70:29–31)

The word's appearance in verse 55 is precise and it is doing specific work. The act is described not as a duty, a custom, or a social arrangement but as driven by *shahwah* — appetite. This distinguishes it from the institution the verse names next.

**Min Dūni'l-Nisāʾ: The Substitution**

*"Instead of women."* The phrase *min dūn* — discussed at verses 24 and 43 as the Qur'an's formula for what is taken in place of God — is used here in its ordinary spatial sense: other than, apart from, in place of. The men of Lot's people approach men *in place of* women.

The Qur'an's argument here rests on the order of creation, and al-Rāzī states it explicitly in the passage the source commentary records: the act went against the Wisdom of God, Who did not create men sexually for men. The argument is developed at length in the parallel accounts:

> *"Verily you approach men with desire instead of women. Nay, but you are an excessing people (musrifūn)."* (Qur'an 7:81)

> *"Verily you approach men instead of women."* — and: *"And Lot — remember when he said unto his people: 'Verily you commit such indecency as no one among the worlds has committed before you.'"* (Qur'an 29:28)

> *"What! Do you approach males among the worlds, and leave what your Lord created for you as spouses? Nay, but you are an excessing people."* (Qur'an 26:165–166)

Verse 26:166 is the fullest statement of the argument, and it has two components. The first is *precedent*: "no one among the worlds has committed this before you" — the act is presented as unprecedented in human history, an innovation in wrongdoing rather than a variation in practice. The second is *function*: "you leave what your Lord created for you as spouses" — the act is presented as a substitution, the abandonment of a created arrangement in favor of something the creation was not ordered toward.

*Isrāf* — excess — is the word both 7:81 and 26:166 use for the diagnosis, and it is from *s-r-f*, going beyond the proper limit, wastefulness, extravagance. The *musrif* is one who exceeds a set boundary. The Qur'an uses the word of wastefulness in spending (6:141, 7:31), of excess in killing (17:33), and of the people of Lot here. The diagnosis is not that they desired too much but that they desired *outside the limit that was set*, which is the same diagnosis verse 12's *fāsiqīn* carried at a different scale.

**Bal Antum Qawmun Tajhalūn: The Diagnosis**

*"Nay, but you are an ignorant people."* The particle *bal* strikes out what precedes and substitutes the correction, as at verses 36 and 47. The verb *tajhalūn* is from *j-h-l*, ignorance — the same root as *jāhiliyyah*, the pre-Islamic Age of Ignorance, *jahl*, ignorance, and *jāhil*, ignorant.

The Qur'an's concept of *jahl* is not the absence of information, and verse 55 demonstrates this. These people know exactly what they are doing — verse 54 said *while you see*. They are not misinformed, not confused, not unaware of Lot's position. Their *jahl* is something else: the failure to know *consequences, limits, and what a thing is for*. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī explain the ignorance here as ignorance of God's punishment and of the outcome of the act; al-Rāzī develops it as the absence of the knowledge that governs appetite.

Arabic distinguishes several kinds of not-knowing, and the tradition's analysis is precise. *Jahl basīṭ* is simple ignorance — not having information. *Jahl murakkab* is compound ignorance — believing one knows what one does not. And *jahl* in the moral sense is the failure of the practical intellect: knowing a rule and not knowing how to live by it. Verse 55's diagnosis is the third kind, and verse 54 has already excluded the first: they see.

**The Sequence of the Two Verses as a Complete Argument**

Verses 54 and 55 together form the Qur'an's most compact statement of its position, and the sequence is worth setting out because it is the sequence the tradition follows.

The act is **classified**: *fāḥishah* — an excess beyond all bounds, in the Qur'an's fixed moral vocabulary (v. 54).

The **defense is removed**: *wa antum tubṣirūn* — you do it seeing, publicly, knowingly (v. 54).

The act is **specified**: approaching men with desire in place of women (v. 55).

The **ground** is given: it is a substitution for what God created as spouses (26:166), unprecedented among the worlds (7:80), and against the wisdom of creation (al-Rāzī).

The **diagnosis** is delivered: you are an ignorant people — not lacking information but lacking the knowledge that governs appetite (v. 55).

Every element is moral and epistemic rather than merely legal. The Qur'an's argument is not primarily that the act is prohibited — though it is, and the legal tradition derives the prohibition from these verses and from the Prophet's ﷺ statements — but that it is *disordered*: excessive, unprecedented, substitutive, public, and the product of a specific kind of ignorance.

**The Legal Tradition's Derivation**

The juristic consequence of these verses was worked out by all four schools, and the derivation rests on the Qur'anic account combined with Prophetic reports. Al-Tirmidhī, Abū Dāwūd, and Ibn Mājah record:

> *"Whoever you find doing the act of the people of Lot, kill the one who does it and the one to whom it is done."* (al-Tirmidhī 1456; Abū Dāwūd 4462; Ibn Mājah 2561)

The scholars discuss the report's chain and its conditions extensively, and the four schools differ on the penalty and on the evidentiary requirements — the Ḥanafīs on the mode of execution, the Mālikīs on stoning for the *muḥṣan*, the Shāfiʿīs on treating it as zinā or as a distinct ḥadd, the Ḥanbalīs on killing both parties. What they do not differ on is the prohibition, which is established by the Qur'an's account across eight sūrahs and by the consensus (*ijmāʿ*) of the ummah from the Companions onward.

The evidentiary conditions the schools impose are the same stringent conditions applied to zinā: four witnesses to the act itself, or a repeated voluntary confession, with the possibility of retraction and with severe penalties for unproven accusation (*qadhf*, 24:4). The legal tradition's severity about the act is matched by its severity about the proof, and the two together define the position: the act is condemned categorically, and no individual may be accused of it without evidence that is almost impossible to produce.

**What Verse 55 Is Not**

It is worth stating what the verse does not do, because misreadings of it in both directions are common.

It does not describe a class of persons. It describes an act — *taʾtūna*, "you approach," a verb of doing — and it addresses a community performing that act publicly, in their gatherings (29:29), while cutting off the road (29:29), and in the face of a prophet's explicit warning. The Qur'an's subject here is a people and their practice, not a taxonomy of human beings.

It does not license any person to injure, insult, or expel another. The response to the act, in the Qur'an's own account, is prophetic warning followed by divine punishment — and the punishment is executed by God, not by the community. Lot is commanded to leave, not to enforce. The Prophet ﷺ's own practice in Madinah with regard to individuals known for effeminacy (*mukhannathūn*) involved banishment from the city rather than the penalties recorded for the act itself, and the jurists' discussion of that practice shows the tradition's care to distinguish the act from a person's manner.

And it does not treat the matter as one on which the Qur'an is silent or ambiguous. Eight sūrahs narrate the same account with the same vocabulary, and the tradition has never read them otherwise. The reader who wishes to know the Qur'an's position has it stated plainly, with a specification, a ground, and a diagnosis — and verse 56 will show what the community did with it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:56

> **Yet the reply of his people was naught but to say, "Expel the family of Lot from your town! Truly they are people who keep themselves pure!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer That Was Not an Answer**

*Fa-mā kāna jawāba qawmihī illā an qālū akhrijū āla Lūṭin min qaryatikum innahum unāsun yataṭahharūn* — "But the answer of his people was nothing except that they said: 'Expel the family of Lot from your town! Truly they are people who keep themselves pure!'"

The verse is one of the Qur'an's most economical descriptions of how a community responds to a moral demand it will not accept. The structure — *fa-mā kāna jawābu qawmihī illā an qālū* — is a negative construction with an exception: "the answer of his people was *nothing except* that they said." The formula states that there was no argument, no reply, no engagement with the content of verses 54–55. The only answer produced was an expulsion order.

**Jawāb: The Word That Was Not Given**

*Jawāb* is from *j-w-b*, to answer, to respond, to traverse — the same root as *ajāba*, to respond, and *istajāba*, to answer a call. The Qur'an uses the word of a real answer:

> *"And Our messenger said: 'Verily God will send upon you a punishment, and you will not be able to avert it.'"* — and the response formula at 21:45

> *"And they said: 'O Shuʿayb, we understand not much of what thou sayest'"* (Qur'an 11:91)

Verse 56 says the answer was *nothing but* the expulsion. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī note the implication: a community that has no answer to a moral claim answers it with force. The absence of argument is not an accident of the narrative; it is the narrative's content.

The Qur'an describes the same phenomenon elsewhere:

> *"And those who disbelieve say: 'Do not listen to this Qur'an, and make noise during it, that haply you may overcome.'"* (Qur'an 41:26)

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'Follow what God has sent down,' they say: 'Nay, we follow what we found our fathers upon.'"* (Qur'an 2:170)

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'Come unto what God has sent down, and unto the Messenger,' they say: 'Sufficient for us is what we found our fathers upon.'"* (Qur'an 5:104)

Verse 41:26 is the clearest parallel: the strategy is not refutation but noise, and its stated purpose is *laʿallakum taghlibūn* — "that haply you may overcome." A community that cannot answer a claim attempts to drown it. Lot's people attempted to remove the claimant.

**Akhirijū Āla Lūṭin Min Qaryatikum: The Order**

*"Expel the family of Lot from your town."* The imperative plural *akhrijū* — "you (all) expel" — is addressed to the community collectively, and the object is *āla Lūṭ* — the family, the household, the people belonging to Lot.

*Āl* is from *ʾ-w-l* or *ʾ-h-l*, family, household, the people of a man — related to *ahl*, discussed at verse 7, where Moses spoke to his *ahl*. The *āl* includes the household and, by extension, the followers and adherents. The order is therefore not directed at Lot alone but at everyone associated with him.

*Qaryatikum* — "your town" — is significant in its possessive. The speakers say *your* town, not *our* town. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī note the possessive as an appeal: they are addressing the mob in the language of shared ownership, mobilizing the community against a household by framing the household as an intrusion into what belongs to everyone else. The same rhetorical move is made in every expulsion in history — the expelled are described as not really belonging to the place that is *ours*.

The parallel account gives the same order in the same words:

> *"But the answer of his people was naught save that they said: 'Expel them from your town! Truly they are people who keep themselves pure!'"* (Qur'an 7:82)

> *"They said: 'If thou desistest not, O Lot, thou wilt surely be among the expelled.'"* (Qur'an 26:167)

Verse 26:167 adds the explicit threat against Lot personally, with the same verb — *mukhrajūn*, "among the expelled." The two verses together show the escalation: first the family, then the prophet himself.

**Innahum Unāsun Yataṭahharūn: The Reason Given**

*"Truly they are people who keep themselves pure."* The stated justification for the expulsion is the household's purity, and the verse's irony is complete.

*Yataṭahharūn* is Form V from *ṭ-h-r*, purity, cleanliness — the same root as *ṭahārah*, ritual and moral purity, *ṭāhir*, pure, *muṭahhir*, purifying, and *taṭhīr*, purification. Form V is reflexive and effortful: they *make themselves* pure, they practice purification, they keep themselves clean.

The Qur'an uses the word as a term of high praise elsewhere, and the source commentary records the contrast: *taṭahhara* is in other contexts a term of praise for those who believe:

> *"And they ask thee concerning menstruation. Say: 'It is a harm, so keep away from women during menstruation, and do not approach them until they are pure (yaṭhurna); then when they have purified themselves (taṭahharna), come unto them as God has commanded you.' Verily God loves those who turn in repentance, and He loves those who purify themselves (al-mutaṭahhirīn)."* (Qur'an 2:222)

> *"Therein are men who love to purify themselves (yāḥibbūna an yataṭahharū), and God loves those who purify themselves."* (Qur'an 9:108)

> *"Do not stand in it ever. A mosque founded upon reverence from the first day is more worthy that thou stand in it. In it are men who love to purify themselves, and God loves those who purify themselves."* (Qur'an 9:108)

> *"Take alms from their wealth, whereby thou mayest purify them and make them grow"* (Qur'an 9:103)

The Qur'an states that God *loves* those who purify themselves. Lot's people state the same fact — they purify themselves — and draw the opposite conclusion: therefore expel them.

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī both explain the pejorative force of the word in verse 56: it means "they are trying to keep themselves pure *from our sexual practices*" — the purity is understood as abstention from what the community does, and the abstention is treated as an insult. The implication the speakers intend is that the household's purity is a judgment on everyone else, and that the judgment is intolerable.

**The Sociology of the Verse: Purity as Provocation**

Verse 56 is one of the Qur'an's most acute social observations, and the observation is general. A community practicing something openly, in its gatherings, with its eyes open (v. 54), does not experience a dissenting household as merely different. It experiences it as *accusatory*. The existence of people who abstain is, by itself, a standing statement that the abstention is possible — and that statement contradicts the community's own justification, which is always that the practice is natural, universal, and unavoidable.

The mechanism is named precisely in the Qur'an's own vocabulary elsewhere:

> *"And those who disbelieve say unto those who believe: 'Follow our way, and we shall bear your sins' — yet they bear nothing of their sins. Verily they are liars."* (Qur'an 29:12)

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'Revere God,' his pride carries him off to sin (akhadhathu'l-ʿizzatu bi'l-ithm)."* (Qur'an 2:206)

> *"And they will not cease to fight you until they turn you back from your religion, if they are able."* (Qur'an 2:217)

Verse 2:206 is the closest parallel in mechanism: the pride (*ʿizzah*, the same root as *ʿuluww* at v. 14 and *al-ʿAzīz* at v. 9) seizes the person *because of* the sin. The sinner's pride is engaged by his sin, and the engagement produces hostility toward whoever reminds him of it. Lot's household reminded the town simply by existing in it.

**The Expulsion as the Community's Real Confession**

There is a final point about the verse that the commentators draw out. The expulsion order is, functionally, an admission. A community that believes its practice is legitimate and defensible does not need to remove the people who dissent from it; it can argue with them, outvote them, or ignore them. The demand for removal is the demand of a community that cannot tolerate the *presence* of a counter-example, because the counter-example is unanswerable.

This is why verse 56 is the structural counterpart of verse 14. Pharaoh's people rejected signs they were certain of, *wrongfully and exultantly*; Lot's people expelled a household whose only offense was purity. In both cases the community's action is not a response to an argument but a response to a *presence* — the presence of truth that cannot be refuted and must therefore be removed.

The Qur'an states the divine evaluation of both:

> *"So behold how was the end of their plotting"* (v. 51) — and: *"And We poured down upon them a rain — evil is the rain of those who were warned"* (v. 58).

**The Verse's Application: The Position of the Pure in an Impure Town**

Verse 56 describes the position of every believing minority in every society, and the description is not comforting. The household of Lot was not persecuting anyone, not proselytizing aggressively, not holding office. It was *keeping itself pure* in a town that had decided purity was an insult.

The verse therefore supplies the believer with three pieces of practical information.

First, that the hostility he encounters may have nothing to do with what he says and everything to do with what he *is*. Lot's people did not answer his argument; they answered his existence. A person who expects his good conduct to be received as good conduct has not read this verse.

Second, that the accusation will be framed in the language of the accuser's own values. They did not say "you are disgusting"; they said "they are people who keep themselves pure" — using the community's own vocabulary of purity as an insult. Every age inverts its predecessors' virtues into its own pejoratives, and the believer should expect the specific virtue he practices to be the one named as his offense.

Third, that the outcome is not in the town's hands. Verse 57 will save the household and verse 58 will destroy the town, and the sūrah's own refrain — *behold how was the end* — applies to expulsions as it applies to conspiracies. The household that was told to leave was the household that was saved, and the town that expelled it was the town that was rained upon.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:57

> **So We saved him and his family, except for his wife. We decreed that she should be among those who lagged behind.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Salvation, and Its One Exception**

*Fa-anjaynāhu wa ahlahū illā imraʾatahu qaddarnāhā mina'l-ghābirīn* — "So We saved him and his family, except his wife; We decreed her to be among those who lagged behind."

The verse mirrors verse 53 in its structure and its verb — *fa-anjaynā*, "so We saved" — and it introduces the exception that makes the Lot narrative one of the Qur'an's most discussed accounts of a household.

**Anjaynāhu Wa Ahlahū: The Household Saved**

*"We saved him and his family."* *Ahl* is the word discussed at verse 7 — the household, the people belonging to a man. The same word appears in the expulsion order of verse 56: *āla Lūṭ*, the family of Lot. The town ordered the family out; God saved the family out. The two expulsions are identical in form and opposite in effect, and the verse's placement immediately after verse 56 makes the reader see both at once.

The parallel accounts give the fuller details:

> *"So We saved him and his family, all of them, except an old woman among those who lagged behind."* (Qur'an 26:170–171)

> *"And when Our command came, We saved Lot and his family by a mercy from Us, and We saved them from the disgrace of that Day."* — cf. 11:66 (of Ṣāliḥ) and the Lot account at 11:81: *"Travel with thy family in a watch of the night, and let none of you turn back — save thy wife; verily what befalls them shall befall her."*

> *"And Lot's daughters [i.e., the women of the town] — he said: 'O my people, here are my daughters; they are purer for you'"* (Qur'an 11:78)

Verse 11:81 supplies the timing and the condition of the escape: travel by night, do not look back, and the wife is excluded. Verse 26:171 identifies her as *ʿajūz*, an old woman, and includes her among *al-ghābirīn*.

**Illā Imraʾatahu: The Exception**

*"Except his wife."* The exception is stated without explanation in this verse, and the Qur'an supplies the explanation elsewhere:

> *"And We made her [or: it] a sign — verily she was of those who lagged behind"* — and the explicit statement: *"So We saved him and his family except an old woman"* — and: *"His wife was one of those who lagged behind"* (Qur'an 7:83, 15:60, 26:171, 29:33, 37:135)

Verse 11:81 gives the reason in God's own words: *"verily what befalls them shall befall her"* — *innahu muṣībuhā mā aṣābahum*. She belonged to the town in the matter that mattered. And the Qur'an's account of her conduct is explicit in the Ḥijr narrative:

> *"And We came down upon them with a rain; and evil was the rain of those who were warned"* — and the report from the commentators, recorded by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr, that she informed the townspeople of Lot's guests.

The tradition's assessment of her is consistent: she was in sympathy with her people's practice, she collaborated with them, and her marriage to a prophet did not save her. The Qur'an makes her one of its two paradigm cases of a disbelieving wife of a believing husband:

> *"God sets forth an example for those who disbelieve: the wife of Noah and the wife of Lot. They were under two of Our righteous servants, and they betrayed them; so they availed them not against God at all, and it was said: 'Enter the Fire with those who enter.'"* (Qur'an 66:10)

*"They betrayed them"* — *fa-khānatāhumā* — and the commentators explain the betrayal not as sexual infidelity, which no prophet's wife committed, but as betrayal of the mission: Noah's wife told people her husband was mad, and Lot's wife informed his people of his guests. Ibn Kathīr states this explicitly and cites the consensus that no prophet's wife was ever unchaste, because such a thing would disgrace the prophet himself.

**Qaddarnāhā: The Decree**

*"We decreed her."* The verb *qaddarnā* is Form II from *q-d-r*, to measure, to apportion, to decree, to determine — the same root as *qadar*, divine decree, *qudrah*, power, and *taqdīr*, apportionment. The Qur'an uses this verb of God's determining all things:

> *"And He created everything and apportioned it with exact apportionment (fa-qaddarahu taqdīran)."* (Qur'an 25:2)

> *"Verily We created everything by decree (bi-qadar)."* (Qur'an 54:49)

> *"And there is not a thing but that its treasures are with Us, and We send it not down save by a known measure."* (Qur'an 15:21)

The use of *qaddarnāhā* here is significant theologically. The wife's fate is described as *decreed* — determined by God's apportionment — and the decree is stated as an act of God in the first person plural. At the same time, verse 66:10 states that she betrayed, and verse 11:81 states that what befell the town befell her because of what she was. The Qur'an holds both statements together without resolving them into one another, and the Muslim theological tradition's position on the relation between decree and choice is built on precisely such juxtapositions.

The classical formulation, developed by al-Ashʿarī, al-Māturīdī, and Ibn Taymiyyah, holds that God's decree encompasses all things including human acts, and that the human being nonetheless acts by his own choice and is accountable for it. The Qur'an's own statements support both halves: *"And you will not, unless God wills"* (76:30) and *"Whoever does righteousness, it is for his own soul; and whoever does evil, it is against it"* (41:46). Verse 57's *qaddarnāhā* is the first half; verse 66:10's *khānatāhumā* is the second.

**Mina'l-Ghābirīn: Those Who Lagged Behind**

*"Among those who lagged behind."* *Ghābir* is the active participle of *gh-b-r*, and the word is one of the Qur'an's most discussed antonyms. Its root has two opposite senses in Arabic: to pass away and be gone, and to remain and linger. The *ghābir* can mean the one who has passed and perished, or the one who remains behind.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī record both readings and explain that in this context the meaning is those who remained behind — those who stayed in the town and perished with it. The word is used of Lot's wife in five places (7:83, 15:60, 26:171, 27:57, 29:33) and in 15:60 and 27:57 as the object of divine decree.

The sense of remaining behind is the more precise, and it carries a moral weight the verse intends. Lot's wife did not merely die; she *lagged*. Verse 11:81 commanded the family not to turn back, and the reports state that she looked behind her — an act the commentators interpret both literally (she turned and was struck) and morally (her heart remained with the town). Either way, the word *ghābir* describes her exactly: she was the one who stayed behind when the rest went forward.

The Qur'an uses the same image of lagging elsewhere, and always with moral force:

> *"And verily there are those among you who lag behind (la-man yubaṭṭiʾu), and if a calamity befalls you, he says: 'God has favored me in that I was not present with them.'"* (Qur'an 4:72)

> *"Then they said to their prophet: 'Send us a king, that we may fight in the way of God'"* — and the description of those who turned away after the fighting was enjoined (Qur'an 2:246–247)

Verse 4:72 is the closest parallel in spirit: the laggard is one who holds back from a collective duty and congratulates himself on his absence when it goes badly. Lot's wife is the inverse — she held back from a collective *salvation*, and her absence was her destruction.

**The Household as the Unit of Salvation**

Verse 57's structure — him and his family, except his wife — makes a theological statement about the units in which God saves. Salvation in the Qur'an is individual in accountability:

> *"And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another."* (Qur'an 6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38)

> *"And every soul earns not [wrong] except against itself, and no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another."* (Qur'an 6:164)

And yet the narratives consistently describe salvation in household terms — Noah's family (11:40), Lot's family (v. 57), the believers among Pharaoh's people, the household of the Prophet ﷺ. The two principles are reconciled by the exception itself: Lot's household was saved *except* the one member whose own conduct placed her elsewhere. The household is the unit of God's mercy; the individual remains the unit of accountability; and no relation to a prophet substitutes for the individual's own position.

Verse 66:10 states the principle in its most explicit form: the two women were *under two of Our righteous servants*, and their proximity *availed them nothing*. Being married to a prophet, being born into a religious family, being raised in a believing household — none of these transfers faith. And the Qur'an's companion case at 66:11 shows the reverse: Pharaoh's wife, inside the worst household in the Qur'an, prays for a house in the Garden and is saved.

**The Verse's Application**

The application of verse 57 is the one every reader in a mixed household or a mixed society needs. Salvation arrived at Lot's house as a specific command with specific conditions: leave tonight, do not look back, and one of you is not coming. The conditions were not negotiable, and the one member who did not meet them was not saved by her marriage.

The practical question the verse puts to a reader is the question of direction. Lot's family walked out of the town at night and did not turn around. The wife lagged. In every life there is a town one must leave — a practice, a relationship, an environment, a version of oneself — and the leaving has the same conditions: go now, and do not keep looking back. The people who perish in the Qur'an's accounts are rarely the ones who refused to leave; they are the ones who left while looking, who kept one foot in the town, whose hearts remained among *al-ghābirīn*.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:58

> **And We poured down upon them a rain—evil is the rain of those who were warned!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Punishment Described as Weather**

*Wa amṭarnā ʿalayhim maṭaran fa-sāʾa maṭaru'l-mundharīn* — "And We rained upon them a rain; and evil is the rain of those who were warned!"

The verse closes the Lot narrative in nine Arabic words, and its central image is deliberately inverted. Rain in the Qur'an is the paradigm of mercy — the thing God sends down to revive a dead land, the image used throughout for revelation itself. Here the same word is used for destruction, and the verse marks the inversion with an exclamation: *fa-sāʾa maṭaru'l-mundharīn* — "and how evil is the rain of those who were warned."

**Amṭarnā ʿAlayhim Maṭaran: The Cognate Accusative**

*"We rained upon them a rain."* The construction uses the cognate accusative — the verbal noun *maṭar* after its own verb *amṭarnā* — for intensification, as at verses 50 and 51. The effect is: we rained upon them *a raining*, rain indeed, a rain of a specific and terrible kind.

The verb *amṭara* is Form IV from *m-ṭ-r*, rain. Arabic distinguishes two verbs: *maṭara*, which is neutral and often used of beneficial rain, and *amṭara*, Form IV, which the classical lexicons and the Qur'an's own usage associate predominantly with rain of punishment or calamity. Al-Rāghib notes the distinction, and the Qur'an's usage bears it out:

> *"And We rained upon them a rain; and evil was the rain of those who were warned."* (Qur'an 27:58)

> *"And We rained upon them a rain from the sky, because they were dissolute."* — cf. 7:84

> *"And We sent down upon them a rain from the sky"* — and the Ḥijr account: *"So We made its highest part its lowest, and We rained upon them stones of baked clay (sijjīl). Verily in that are signs for those who discern."* (Qur'an 15:74–75)

> *"And when a wave covers them like a canopy, they call upon God, sincere in faith to Him; but when He brings them safe to land, some of them are moderate"* — and: *"and We rained upon them stones"* — cf. 8:32, where the disbelievers of Makkah ask for it: *"O God, if this is the truth from Thee, then rain down upon us (fa-amṭir ʿalaynā) stones from the sky."*

By contrast, the beneficial rain is usually given with the verb *anzala* or *nazzala*:

> *"And We sent down from the sky blessed water, and We made grow therewith gardens and grain harvested."* (Qur'an 50:9)
