> *"And He it is Who sends down the rain (ghayth) after they have despaired, and spreads His mercy."* (Qur'an 42:28)

Verse 58's use of *amṭarnā* with the cognate *maṭar* therefore carries, in the Qur'an's own idiom, the sense of a rain that is calamity.

**The Manner of the Punishment in the Parallel Accounts**

Al-Naml's account is the most compressed of the Qur'an's eight narrations, and the parallel accounts supply the detail:

> *"So when Our command came, We made its highest part its lowest (jaʿalnā ʿāliyahā sāfilahā), and We rained upon them stones of baked clay (ḥijāratan min sijjīl), layered, marked with thy Lord; and they are not far from the wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 11:82–83)

> *"Verily We sent upon them a single cry (ṣayḥatan wāḥidah), and they became like the dry twigs of a fence-builder."* — cf. the Thamūd parallel at 54:31

> *"And We made it [the towns] an upside-down [thing], and We rained upon them stones of clay."* — cf. 15:74

> *"And the cities of Lot he overthrew (al-muʾtafikāt), so there covered them what covered them."* (Qur'an 53:53–54)

> *"And [mention] the overthrown cities (al-muʾtafikah) — He covered them with what covered them."* (Qur'an 53:53)

The elements across the accounts are: an overturning of the towns (their high made low), a rain of stones of baked clay (*sijjīl*), marked and layered, and the removal of Lot's household beforehand. The tradition reconciles the accounts as successive moments of a single event, and the Qur'an's own summary in al-Naml — "we rained upon them a rain" — is the general term covering all of it.

**Fa-Sāʾa Maṭaru'l-Mundharīn: The Exclamation**

*"And how evil is the rain of those who were warned!"* The verb *sāʾa* is from *s-w-ʾ*, badness — the same root as *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb* at verse 5 ("a terrible punishment"). In this construction, *sāʾa* functions as a verb of censure and wretchedness: "how evil it is, how wretched." Arabic uses the construction *biʾsa* and *sāʾa* for emphatic dispraise, and the Qur'an uses it frequently:

> *"Evil is that which they have done."* — cf. 5:66, 9:9, 63:2

> *"Evil is the place of rest (biʾsa'l-mahād)"* — cf. 3:12, 162, 2:206

> *"And evil is the rain of those who were warned."* (Qur'an 27:58)

The source commentary notes the parallel at 25:40, where the Qur'an says of the destroyed peoples: *"And they passed by the town that was rained upon with an evil rain (bi-maṭari'l-sawʾ)"* — using the same phrase with the noun *sawʾ* explicitly. The two verses together establish the phrase as a fixed formula for the punishment of Lot's people.

**Al-Mundharīn: Those Who Were Warned**

*"Of those who were warned."* *Mundhar* is the passive participle of Form IV from *n-dh-r*, to warn, to give notice of danger — the same root as *nadhīr*, a warner, and *indhār*, warning. The Qur'an uses the word of the function of every prophet:

> *"And there is no community but that a warner (nadhīr) has passed among them."* (Qur'an 35:24)

> *"That thou mayest warn a people whose fathers were not warned, so they are heedless."* (Qur'an 36:6)

> *"Verily I am unto you a clear warner (nadhīrun mubīn)."* (Qur'an 11:2; 15:89; 51:50–51)

The word's appearance in the exclamation is the verse's moral center. The rain was evil *because* the people rained upon had been warned. The warning is what converts a catastrophe into a punishment: a disaster that falls on the unwarned is a tragedy; a disaster that falls on the warned is a *consequence*.

The Qur'an states the principle as a rule of divine justice:

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

> *"Messengers, bearers of good news and warning, so that mankind should have no argument against God after the messengers."* (Qur'an 4:165)

> *"And thy Lord would not destroy the towns unjustly while their people were reformers."* (Qur'an 11:117)

Verse 58 is the principle applied: Lot warned (vv. 54–55), the town answered with expulsion (v. 56), the household was removed (v. 57), and the rain fell (v. 58) — upon *al-mundharīn*, the warned ones, whose warning had been delivered, received, answered, and exhausted.

**The Rain as the Sūrah's Fourth Separation**

Verse 58 closes the fourth of al-Naml's narrative case studies, and the closing follows the pattern established by the previous three. Each case presents a demand, a set of responses, and a separation:

Moses and Pharaoh (vv. 7–14): the demand is the nine signs; the response is "manifest sorcery" and rejection despite certainty; the separation is drowning, and the refrain is *"behold how the workers of corruption fared in the end."*

Solomon and Bilqīs (vv. 15–44): the demand is *"do not exalt yourselves against me, but come unto me in submission"*; the response is consultation, a gift, a journey, and a confession; the separation is voluntary, and the outcome is submission.

Ṣāliḥ and Thamūd (vv. 45–53): the demand is *"worship God"*; the response is augury and a nine-man conspiracy; the separation is destruction and salvation, and the refrain is *"behold how their plot fared in the end."*

Lot and his town (vv. 54–58): the demand is *"do you commit indecency while you see?"*; the response is an expulsion order; the separation is the household's night departure and the town's rain.

The four cases cover the full range of responses to a prophet — refusal by a tyrant, acceptance by a sovereign, conspiracy by an elite, expulsion by a majority — and the sūrah's argument is complete. What follows (vv. 59–64) is the theological conclusion: five questions that remove the possibility of any further excuse.

**The Verse's Application: The Weight of Having Been Warned**

The practical force of verse 58 lies in its last word. The evil of the rain was not in its volume or its violence. It was in the identity of the people it fell on: *al-mundharīn*, the warned.

Every reader of the Qur'an is, by the act of reading, among the warned. This is not a rhetorical point but a legal one in the Qur'an's own framework: warning creates responsibility, and responsibility creates liability. A person who has never heard is in a different category from a person who has heard and answered with an expulsion order. The Qur'an states the categories explicitly:

> *"That I may warn you thereby, and whomever it reaches."* (Qur'an 6:19)

> *"And this Qur'an has been revealed unto me that I may warn you thereby, and whomever it reaches."* — and the tradition records the implication: whoever it reaches is warned, and whoever is warned is *mundhar*.

The verse therefore ends the Lot narrative with a word that applies to everyone reading it. The rain fell on the warned. The warning has now reached you. And the sūrah, having presented four case studies of what communities did with their warnings, is about to ask five questions that no reader can answer without deciding which case study he is in.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:59

> **Say, "Praise be to God, and peace be upon His servants whom He has chosen." Is God better, or the partners they ascribe?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn From Narrative to Argument**

*Quli'l-ḥamdu li'Llāhi wa salāmun ʿalā ʿibādihī'lladhīna ṣṭafā ā-Allāhu khayrun ammā yushrikūn* — "Say: 'Praise be to God, and peace be upon His servants whom He has chosen. Is God better, or that which they associate?'"

With verse 59 the sūrah leaves narrative and enters its theological center. The four case studies are complete; the four communities have answered; and the Qur'an now turns to address the reader directly, in the imperative, in the second person: *qul* — "say." The word appears three times in this section (vv. 59, 64, 65) and marks the passages in which the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to speak on the Qur'an's behalf rather than to narrate.

**Quli'l-Ḥamdu Li'Llāh: Praise as the Opening of Argument**

The command to speak begins with praise, and the beginning is not a formality. *Al-ḥamd* — the praise — is the Qur'an's first substantive word (1:2) and its prescribed opening for every significant act. Here it opens a passage of argument, and the opening is itself part of the argument.

The commentators explain the placement. After four narratives ending in destruction — Pharaoh drowned, Thamūd's houses fallen, Lot's town rained upon — and one ending in submission, the correct response is not triumph but praise. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the ḥamd here is gratitude for the demonstration of God's justice and power across the preceding stories, and praise that He is as He has been described.

There is also a polemical function. The argument that follows is against *shirk*, and the argument begins by praising the One who is to be argued for. The effect is that the reader enters the argument already in the posture of worship. This is characteristic of the Qur'an's method: it does not argue with a neutral reader, because there is no neutral reader. It argues with a reader who has just been made to praise.

**Wa Salāmun ʿAlā ʿIbādihī'lladhīna Ṣṭafā: Peace Upon the Chosen Servants**

*"And peace upon His servants whom He has chosen."* *Salām* is from *s-l-m*, safety, soundness, peace — the same root as *islām* and *muslim*, discussed at verse 31, and as *salāmah*, wholeness and freedom from defect. *Salām ʿalā* is the formula of greeting and of benediction, and the Qur'an uses it of God's address to His messengers:

> *"And peace be upon the messengers, and praise be to God, Lord of the worlds."* (Qur'an 37:181–182)

> *"Peace be upon Noah among the worlds."* (Qur'an 37:79)

> *"Peace be upon Abraham."* (Qur'an 37:109)

> *"Peace be upon Moses and Aaron."* (Qur'an 37:120)

> *"Peace be upon the family of Yāsīn."* (Qur'an 37:130)

The parallel with 37:181–182 is exact: both passages close a series of prophetic narratives with praise of God and peace upon His servants. Al-Naml's version is placed at the *beginning* of its argumentative section rather than at the end of the sūrah, and it thereby does double duty — closing the four narratives and opening the five questions.

**Alladhīna Ṣṭafā: The Verb of Choosing**

*"Whom He has chosen."* *Iṣṭafā* is Form VIII from *ṣ-f-w*, purity, clarity, freedom from admixture — the same root as *ṣafw*, the purest part of a thing, and *ṣafwah*, the chosen elite. Form VIII adds the sense of selecting for oneself: *iṣṭafā* means "he chose and took for himself," to select the pure from among the mixed.

The Qur'an uses this verb of God's election, and always in the same theological register:

> *"Verily God chose (iṣṭafā) Adam and Noah and the family of Abraham and the family of ʿImrān above the worlds."* (Qur'an 3:33)

> *"And thy Lord creates what He wills and chooses (yakhtār); the choice is not theirs."* (Qur'an 28:68)

> *"And We chose them (iṣṭafaynāhum) and guided them to a straight path."* (Qur'an 6:87)

> *"God knows best where to place His message."* (Qur'an 6:124)

The choice is God's alone, and the servants chosen are the messengers and those who followed them. The commentators identify the referents here variously: the prophets named in the preceding narratives, the angels, or the believers generally. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr prefer the prophets, given the immediate context of four prophetic stories; al-Qurṭubī allows the broader reading.

The phrase matters for the argument that follows. The chosen servants are the ones who delivered the warnings, and the peace pronounced upon them is a confirmation of their standing. The five questions of verses 60–64 are, in effect, the content of what those chosen servants said — and the verse places their authority before the argument begins.

**A-Allāhu Khayrun Ammā Yushrikūn: The Question That Opens the Series**

*"Is God better, or that which they associate?"* The interrogative *a-* opens a question that expects a single answer, and the answer is so obvious that the Qur'an does not give it. The construction *a-X khayrun am Y* — "is X better or Y?" — is used in Arabic for a comparison in which one term is manifestly superior, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly:

> *"Is the Garden promised to the reverent better, or the Fire?"* — cf. the rhetorical comparison at 13:5 and 41:40

> *"Say: 'Is the blind equal to the seeing? Or are the darknesses equal to the light?'"* (Qur'an 13:16)

> *"Are the ones who know equal to the ones who do not know?"* (Qur'an 39:9)

> *"Is God better, or that which they associate?"* (Qur'an 27:59)

The question is followed by five verses, each of which supplies a reason for the answer, and each of which ends with the refrain *"Is there a god alongside God?"* (a-ilāhun maʿa Llāh). Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both explain the relation: verse 59 poses the comparative question, and verses 60–64 answer it five times, from five different directions, without ever stating the answer directly.

**Yushrikūn: The Verb of Association**

*"That which they associate."* *Ashraka* is Form IV from *sh-r-k*, to share, to partner, to associate — the same root as *sharik*, partner, *shirk*, association, and *mushrik*, one who associates. Form IV means to make something a partner, and the Qur'an uses it exclusively of the association of others with God in worship, lordship, or attributes.

The grammatical construction *ammā yushrikūn* — "or that which they associate" — uses the relative *mā*, "that which," rather than *man*, "the one whom." Al-Zamakhsharī notes the significance: *mā* is used for non-rational referents, and its use here reduces the associated objects to the category of *things* — not persons, not rivals, but objects. The comparison is therefore not between God and other gods; it is between God and objects. The question is not "which god is better?" but "is the Creator better, or the objects?"

This is the Qur'an's consistent treatment of *shirk*: it does not dignify the associated objects by arguing against their divinity, because it does not concede that they are candidates. It argues against their *existence as agents*, and it does so by listing what God does and observing that they do none of it. The five verses that follow are that list.

**The Refrain That Follows: Five Times, Five Directions**

The source commentary explains the structure of the section: verses 59–64 are devoted to descriptions of God in the form of rhetorical questions, ending with the refrain *"Is there a god alongside God?"* The questions beginning *"He, Who"* (*amman khalaqa*, *amman jaʿala*, etc.) are structured to seem as though they will end with a comparison between God and something else, but instead they arrive at the question *"Is there a god alongside God?"* By implication they are asking, in so many different ways, the question posed in verse 59.

The grammar of *amman* deserves analysis. It is a contraction of *a-* (the interrogative) plus *man* (who) plus the implied predicate — literally, "or is He who...?" The construction opens a comparison and leaves it hanging: "Is He who created the heavens and the earth [better than one who did not]?" The implied term is never supplied, and al-Rāzī explains why: the questions are structured so that God's power and beneficence appear so great that one should not even offer an explicit comparison with Him. The comparison would be an insult to the thing compared, so it is suppressed, and the sentence arrives instead at the refrain.

The five verses cover five domains: the sky and its water and the gardens it grows (60); the earth and its rivers, mountains, and seas (61); the prayer of the distressed and the succession of generations (62); guidance in darkness and the winds before the rain (63); and the origination and return of creation with its provision (64). Each domain is observable, each is universally experienced, each is one no associated object can claim — and each ends with the same question.

The refrain *a-ilāhun maʿa Llāh* — "is there a god *alongside* God?" — is the Qur'an's most compressed refutation of *shirk*, and its repetition five times in five verses is unmatched anywhere else in the Book. It is not an argument; it is a question that has no possible affirmative answer, asked five times so that the hearer hears himself failing to answer it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:60

> **He, Who created the heavens and the earth, and sent down water for you from the sky, through which We make grow beautiful gardens, whose trees it is not for you to make grow? Is there a god alongside God? Nay, but they are a people who ascribe equals.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Question: Creation, Water, and Gardens**

*Amman khalaqa'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍa wa anzala lakum mina'l-samāʾi māʾan fa-anbatnā bihī ḥadāʾiqa dhāta bahjatin mā kāna lakum an tunbitū shajarahā a-ilāhun maʿa Llāhi bal hum qawmun yaʿdilūn* — "Or is He who created the heavens and the earth, and sent down for you water from the sky, whereby We caused to grow gardens of beauty — it is not for you to cause their trees to grow? Is there a god alongside God? Nay, but they are a people who set up equals."

**Amman Khalaqa: The Hanging Comparison**

The verse opens with the contracted interrogative *amman* — "or is He who..." — and the comparison it opens is never completed. Al-Rāzī and al-Bayḍāwī explain the device: the sentence promises a second term, delivers instead a list of divine acts, and ends with the refrain. The hearer supplies the missing term himself, and in supplying it he answers the question.

The parallel construction appears in Sūrah al-Raʿd:

> *"Is He Who knows what is sent down unto thee from thy Lord like the one who is blind?"* (Qur'an 13:19)

> *"Or is He Who created the heavens and the earth and sent down water from the sky — whereby We caused to grow gardens of beauty, and it is not for you to cause their trees to grow — is there a god alongside God? Nay, but they are a people who ascribe equals."* — cf. 13:33, which the source commentary cites as the structural parallel

**Khalaqa'l-Samāwāti Wa'l-Arḍ: The Largest Claim First**

*"Who created the heavens and the earth."* The verse begins with the comprehensive act, and the Qur'an's argument throughout is that the creation of the heavens and the earth is a claim no associated object can make:

> *"Verily those whom you call upon apart from God cannot create a fly, even if they gathered together for it; and if the fly snatches something from them, they cannot recover it from him. Weak is the seeker and the sought."* (Qur'an 22:73)

> *"Say: 'Call upon those you suppose, apart from God; they possess not an atom's weight in the heavens or on the earth, nor do they have a share in either, nor is there for Him among them any supporter.'"* (Qur'an 34:22)

> *"Do they not see that the heavens and the earth were joined together, and We split them apart, and We made from water every living thing? Will they not then believe?"* (Qur'an 21:30)

The argument from creation is the Qur'an's first and most universal, and it is addressed to a hearer who already accepts its premise. Al-Ṭabarī notes that even the pagan Arabs acknowledged that God created the heavens and the earth:

> *"And if thou askest them: 'Who created the heavens and the earth?' they will surely say: 'The Mighty, the Knowing created them.'"* (Qur'an 43:9)

> *"And if thou askest them who created them, they will surely say: 'God.' So how are they turned away?"* (Qur'an 43:87)

The verse therefore begins with common ground, and its argument proceeds from what the opponent already grants to what he does not.

**Wa Anzala Lakum Mina'l-Samāʾi Māʾan: Water Sent Down For You**

*"And sent down for you water from the sky."* The pronoun *lakum* — "for you" — is significant and the commentators note it. The water is not sent down in general; it is sent down *for you*, addressed to the hearers, in the second person. The verse shifts from the third-person description of creation to a direct address, and the shift is the argument's engine: this is not a cosmological statement about a distant event; it is a statement about the rain that fell on your fields.

*Anzala* is Form IV from *n-z-l*, to send down, to cause to descend — the same verb used of the revelation (*anzalnā ilayka'l-kitāb*, 5:48) and of iron (*wa anzalnā'l-ḥadīda fīhi baʾsun shadīdun*, 57:25). The Qur'an consistently describes rain as *sent down*, not as produced:

> *"And We sent down from the sky water in measure, and We lodged it in the earth; and verily We are able to take it away."* (Qur'an 23:18)

> *"And God sent down water from the sky, and revived therewith the earth after its death."* (Qur'an 16:65)

Verse 23:18's second clause is the verse's most unsettling addition: the water is lodged in the earth, and God is able to take it away. The dependency runs both ways, and the reader who drinks a glass of water is drinking something sent down and something that could be withdrawn.

**Fa-Anbatnā Bihī Ḥadāʾiqa Dhāta Bahjatin: Gardens of Beauty**

*"Whereby We caused to grow gardens possessing beauty."* The verb *anbatnā* is Form IV from *n-b-t*, to sprout, to grow, to vegetate — the same root as *nabāt*, plants and vegetation. The Qur'an uses the verb of God's causing growth, and it uses it metaphorically of Mary:

> *"And her Lord accepted her with good acceptance, and made her grow with a good growth (anbatahā nabātan ḥasanan)."* (Qur'an 3:37)

*Ḥadāʾiq* is the plural of *ḥadīqah*, and al-Ṭabarī explains it as walled gardens; al-Qurṭubī adds gardens of palm trees. The distinction between a *ḥadīqah* and an ordinary garden (*jannah*) in the lexicons is that the *ḥadīqah* is enclosed — a garden with a wall around it, which is why the word is used of the intensively cultivated, irrigated gardens of the Arabian oases.

*Bahjah* is from *b-h-j*, brightness, splendor, delight — the same root as *bahija*, to be delighted, and *bahīj*, radiant. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as connoting edible flowers, or simply anything from which human beings and animals eat; the lexical sense is the *delightfulness* of appearance — the green of a garden after rain, which the classical Arabic poets treated as the paradigm of beauty.

The word choice is precise for the argument. The verse is not describing utility alone; it is describing *beauty*. The gardens are *dhāt bahjah* — possessing splendor, delightful to look at. And the argument that follows depends on this: the thing you find most beautiful in the natural world, the green enclosed garden in a dry land, is not something you made.

**Mā Kāna Lakum An Tunbitū Shajarahā: The Denial of Capacity**

*"It is not for you to cause their trees to grow."* The construction *mā kāna lakum an* — "it is not for you to, it was never within your capacity to" — is a strong negation of capability, and the Qur'an uses it elsewhere:

> *"It is not for a prophet that he should have captives until he has made a slaughter in the land."* — cf. 8:67

> *"And it is not for a soul to die save by God's leave, at a term appointed."* (Qur'an 3:145)

> *"It is not for the sun to overtake the moon, nor does the night outstrip the day."* (Qur'an 36:40)

The negated verb is *tunbitū* — "you cause to grow" — Form IV of the same root as *anbatnā* in the preceding clause. The Qur'an sets the two verbs side by side: *We* caused gardens to grow; *you* cannot cause their trees to grow. The same act, the same verb form, two subjects — and the second subject's incapacity is stated absolutely.

Al-Rāzī, as the source commentary notes, observes that this verse disabuses people of the idea that a human being makes a tree grow, when all aspects of its being and survival originate with God. The point is not that human beings do nothing: they dig, they plant, they irrigate, they prune. The point is that none of these acts produces the growth. A person can put a seed in soil and water it for a year and no tree will appear unless something he cannot supply arrives.

The Qur'an makes the identical argument about agriculture elsewhere, in a passage that reads as a commentary on this verse:

> *"Have you considered what you sow? Is it you who cause it to grow, or are We the Causers of growth? If We willed, We would make it chaff, and you would remain wondering: 'Verily we are burdened! Nay, we are deprived!'"* (Qur'an 56:63–67)

> *"Have you considered the water you drink? Is it you who sent it down from the clouds, or are We the Senders? If We willed, We would make it bitter; so why do you not give thanks?"* (Qur'an 56:68–70)

> *"Have you considered the fire you kindle? Is it you who made its tree grow, or are We the Makers?"* (Qur'an 56:71–72)

The three questions of Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah cover the same ground as verse 60 in the same order: seed, water, fire — and each ends with the same structure: *is it you, or is it We?* The parallel is exact enough that the commentators treat the two passages as a single argument presented twice.

**A-Ilāhun Maʿa Llāh: The Refrain**

*"Is there a god alongside God?"* The refrain appears for the first time, and its grammatical form deserves attention. *A-ilāhun maʿa Llāh* — the interrogative *a-*, then *ilāhun* (a god, indefinite), then *maʿa* (with, alongside), then *Allāh*.

The indefinite *ilāhun* is significant: the question is not "is *the* god alongside God?" but "is there *any* god alongside God?" The indefiniteness makes the question exhaustive — no candidate, of any description, is exempt from it.

*Maʿa* — alongside, with, together with — is the preposition of *shirk* in its literal sense: partnership, being with. The question asks whether anything stands *with* God in the acts just listed. And the answer, which the verse has already supplied, is that nothing does: nothing created the heavens and the earth, nothing sent down the water, nothing caused the gardens to grow, and no human being can cause a single tree to grow.

The refrain is repeated five times in verses 60–64, and no other sūrah repeats a sentence so insistently within so few verses. The effect is cumulative rather than argumentative: the first time, the hearer considers the question; the second time, he notices the pattern; by the fifth, the question has become a rhythm, and the absence of an answer has become the answer.

**Bal Hum Qawmun Yaʿdilūn: The Diagnosis**

*"Nay, but they are a people who set up equals."* The verb *yaʿdilūn* is from *ʿ-d-l*, and the source commentary records its double sense: it can mean to set up an equal or counterpart (*ʿadīl*, a peer), and it can mean to swerve or turn away — since the verb changes meaning depending on the preposition that follows it, and here there is no preposition to indicate which shade is emphasized. Both meanings are accepted by the commentators.

The double sense is a single diagnosis from two sides. To set up an equal is to place something alongside God (the act of *shirk*); to swerve is to turn away from Him (the consequence). Al-Zamakhsharī prefers the first reading given the absence of a preposition, which in Arabic typically indicates the sense of making an equal; al-Ṭabarī records both.

*Bal* — "nay, rather" — strikes out an implicit alternative, as at verses 36 and 47. The alternative struck out here is the possibility that the question was not understood. These are not people who failed to hear the argument; they are people who, having heard it, set up equals anyway. Verse 66 will develop this diagnosis: *"Nay, but they are in doubt concerning it; indeed, they are blind to it."*

**The Verse's Application: The Garden You Did Not Make**

Verse 60 is the most immediately verifiable argument in the Qur'an, and it requires no expertise, no travel, and no instrument. The reader is asked to consider three things he has already experienced: a sky, rain, and a plant.

The plant is the decisive term. Every human being who has ever attempted to grow something — a seed in a pot, a cutting in water, a garden in a yard — knows exactly what the verse is saying. You can supply the soil, the water, the light, the warmth, the nutrients, and the protection. You cannot supply the growth. At some point in the process, something happens that you did not do and cannot do: the seed splits, the root descends, the shoot rises, the leaves unfold, and the thing becomes itself. You were present. You were not the agent.

That single observation, which any person can make in a week with a bean and a cup of water, is the whole of verse 60's argument. And the argument's conclusion is not that God exists — most of the verse's original hearers granted that. The conclusion is narrower and more demanding: *if you cannot make a tree grow, what are you doing prostrating to the sun?* The sun does not make the tree grow either. It is a lamp in the sky, and the garden is not a lamp.

The modern reader lives inside a civilization that has made the observation harder, not easier. Food arrives packaged, water arrives in pipes, plants arrive in pots from nurseries, and the entire chain of causation between the rain and the meal has been hidden by infrastructure. Verse 60's argument therefore requires, for a modern hearer, an act of attention that its original hearers did not need to perform: to trace a single meal back through the supermarket, the distributor, the farm, the seed, the soil, the rain, the sky — and to notice where one's own agency ends.

It ends very early. And the refrain is waiting at the end of the trace.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:61

> **He, Who made the earth a dwelling place, and made rivers to run through it, and made firm mountains for it, and made between the two seas a barrier? Is there a god alongside God? Nay, but most of them know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Question: The Earth as a Habitation**

*Amman jaʿala'l-arḍa qarāran wa jaʿala khilālahā anhāran wa jaʿala lahā rawāsiya wa jaʿala bayna'l-baḥrayni ḥājizan a-ilāhun maʿa Llāhi bal aktharuhum lā yaʿlamūn* — "Or is He who made the earth a settled abode, and made rivers in its midst, and made for it firm mountains, and made between the two seas a barrier? Is there a god alongside God? Nay, but most of them know not."

The verse contains four acts, each introduced by the same verb — *jaʿala*, "He made, He appointed, He constituted" — and each describing a feature of the earth that makes human habitation possible. The repetition of the verb four times in one verse is a deliberate rhythm, and the four acts proceed from the general to the specific: the earth as a whole, then water within it, then mountains upon it, then the seas at its edges.

**Jaʿala: The Verb of Constitution**

*Jaʿala* is from *j-ʿ-l*, to make, to place, to appoint, to constitute — and in Arabic grammar it is one of the verbs that takes two objects, transforming one thing into another or appointing one thing to a function. The Qur'an uses it constantly of God's constitutive acts:

> *"It is He Who made (jaʿala) for you the earth a dwelling place and the sky a canopy."* — cf. 40:64

> *"And He made (jaʿala) the darknesses and the light."* (Qur'an 6:1)

> *"And We made (jaʿalnā) the night and the day two signs."* (Qur'an 17:12)

The verb's force is that of *designation with purpose*: God did not merely produce these things; He appointed them to functions. The earth is not accidentally habitable; it was *made* a settled abode.

**Qarāran: The Settled Abode**

*"The earth a settled abode."* *Qarār* is from *q-r-r*, to settle, to be fixed, to come to rest — the same root as *mustaqirran* at verse 40, where the throne was *settled* before Solomon, and *mustaqarr* at 36:38, where the sun runs to its fixed resting place. The word denotes a place of stability and repose, a settled dwelling.

The parallel verse in Sūrah Ghāfir gives the fuller statement:

> *"God is He Who made the earth a dwelling place (qarāran) for you and the sky a structure, and formed you and perfected your forms, and provided you with good things. That is God, your Lord; so blessed be God, Lord of the worlds."* (Qur'an 40:64)

> *"Is He not the One Who made the earth a settled abode (qarāran) and made rivers in its midst and made for it firm mountains and made between the two seas a barrier? Is there a god alongside God? Nay, but most of them know not."* (Qur'an 27:61)

Verse 21:31 supplies the reasoning:

> *"And We made in the earth firmly set mountains, lest it should shake with them, and We made therein broad paths, that haply they might be guided."* (Qur'an 21:31)

> *"And God made the earth a carpet for you, that you may travel therein along broad paths."* — cf. 71:19–20

The *qarār* is thus the earth's stability: its fixedness underfoot, its non-shaking, its suitability as a floor for building, walking, planting, and sleeping. The Qur'an's argument is that this stability is not a trivial feature but a *constituted* one — appointed for habitation, and therefore the work of a maker who intended inhabitants.

**Wa Jaʿala Khilālahā Anhāran: Rivers in Its Midst**

*"And made rivers in its midst."* *Khilālahā* is from *kh-l-l*, the interior, the midst, the spaces between — the same root as *khall*, a cleft or gap, and *khilāl*, the spaces between things. The phrase means rivers *running through* the earth, in its interior spaces, between its parts.

The source commentary notes the parallel at 18:33: *"We made a stream gush forth in their midst"* — the two gardens of the man in Sūrah al-Kahf. And the Qur'an describes the rivers as God's provision repeatedly:

> *"And We made a stream gush forth in their midst (khilālahumā)."* (Qur'an 18:33)

> *"Have they not seen that We drive the water to a parched land, and We bring forth thereby crops whereof their cattle and they themselves eat? Will they not then see?"* (Qur'an 32:27)

> *"And it is He Who has made for you the stars, that you may be guided by them in the darknesses of land and sea."* — cf. 6:97, which verse 63 will echo

The rivers are the distribution system for the water sent down in verse 60, and the connection between the two verses is exact: verse 60 sends down water from the sky and grows gardens; verse 61 puts rivers *in the midst* of the earth so that the water is available where the gardens are not.

**Wa Jaʿala Lahā Rawāsiya: The Firm Mountains**

*"And made for it firm mountains."* *Rawāsī* is the plural of *rāsiyah*, the active participle of *r-s-w*, to be fixed, to be anchored, to settle firmly — the same root as *arsā*, to anchor, and *rawāsī* denotes things fixed and immovable. The Qur'an uses the word of the mountains consistently:

> *"And He cast into the earth firmly set mountains (rawāsiya) lest it shake with you, and rivers and paths, that haply you might be guided."* (Qur'an 16:15)

> *"And We placed therein firmly set mountains (rawāsiya shāmikhāt), and We gave you to drink sweet water."* (Qur'an 77:27)

> *"Have they not considered the earth — how much of every noble pair We caused to grow therein?"* — and: *"And the mountains We fixed them firmly (wa'l-jibāla awtadnāhā)."* (Qur'an 78:7)

> *"And the mountains, He fixed them (arsāhā)."* (Qur'an 79:32)

Verse 78:7's *awtād* — pegs, stakes — is the Qur'an's most concrete image for the mountains' function: they are the pegs that hold the earth's surface in place, as a tent is held by its stakes. The source commentary directs the reader to 21:30–31 for the concept of firm mountains, and the tradition's discussion of the verses has extended into modern debates about isostasy and the roots of mountain ranges — a discussion the classical exegetes could not have had but whose terms they anticipated, since al-Rāzī asks explicitly whether the mountains' weight stabilizes or destabilizes the earth and answers that their distribution does the work.

The argument's function in verse 61 is not geological but teleological: the mountains are *made for* the earth (*lahā*, "for it"), and their making has a purpose related to habitation. The earth is a *qarār* — a settled place — and the mountains are part of what settles it.

**Wa Jaʿala Bayna'l-Baḥrayni Ḥājizan: The Barrier Between the Two Seas**

*"And made between the two seas a barrier."* This is the verse's most discussed clause, and the discussion has continued for fourteen centuries.

*Baḥrayn* is the dual of *baḥr*, sea — "the two seas." The commentators identify them variously: the salt sea and the fresh sea (the ocean and the rivers), the Mediterranean and the Red Sea, the Atlantic and the Indian, or the sea of the heavens and the sea of the earth. The Qur'an uses the same phrase in three other places, and the parallels illuminate it:

> *"And He it is Who has made the two seas to flow, this sweet and fresh, and that salt and bitter; and He has made between them a barrier (barzakh) and a forbidding partition (ḥijran maḥjūrā)."* (Qur'an 25:53)

> *"The two seas are not alike: this is fresh, sweet, and pleasant to drink, and that is salt and bitter. Yet from each you eat fresh meat and extract ornaments that you wear."* (Qur'an 35:12)

> *"He released the two seas, meeting; between them is a barrier (barzakh) they do not transgress. So which of the favors of your Lord will you two deny?"* (Qur'an 55:19–21)

Verse 25:53 identifies the two seas by their water: one sweet and fresh, one salt and bitter. Verse 55:19–20 describes them as *meeting* while a barrier between them is not transgressed. Verse 35:12 notes that both yield fresh meat and ornaments.

The term used for the barrier differs between the passages, and the source commentary notes the distinction: here in al-Naml the word is *ḥājiz* — from *ḥ-j-z*, to separate, to partition, to come between — while in 25:53 and 55:19 the word is *barzakh*, which denotes an isthmus, an intervening space, a partition between two things. *Ḥājiz* is the more active term — a separator, a thing that separates — and *barzakh* the more spatial, a zone between.

The classical exegetes explained the phenomenon in terms available to them: God's power keeps two adjacent bodies of water distinct, so that the salt does not overwhelm the fresh and the fresh does not dilute the salt, and this distinction is maintained despite their meeting. Modern readers have noted the correspondence with the physics of estuaries and of the boundaries between water masses of different salinity and density — a correspondence that Muslim scientists and Qur'anic researchers have discussed extensively since the mid-twentieth century, and which the classical exegetes could describe functionally (the waters do not mix) without the conceptual apparatus to explain the mechanism.

The scholarly caution required here is the same caution al-Rāzī applied to the beast of verse 82: the Qur'an states a fact about the created order and invites reflection on it; it does not provide a technical treatise, and the reader should neither force the text into a modern framework nor deny that the text describes something real. What the verse asserts is that a barrier exists, that God made it, and that its making is a reason to ask whether anything stands alongside God.

**The Sufi Reading: The Two Seas Within**

Al-Rāzī records an interpretation, cited in the source commentary, that reads the two seas allegorically: within the soul there are two seas — the sea of faith and wisdom, and the sea of rebellion and passion — and God by His grace placed a barrier between them, a barrier maintained as long as the soul is not insolent and rebellious toward God.

The allegory is coherent with the plain sense and rests on its essential structure: two bodies that would destroy each other if mixed, held apart by a constituted barrier. The Qur'an uses the same image of the soul's internal division elsewhere:

> *"And We have guided him to the two ways (al-najdayn)."* (Qur'an 90:10)

> *"And a soul and He Who proportioned it, and inspired it with its wickedness and its reverence."* (Qur'an 91:7–8)

> *"Verily the soul is a commander of evil, save that upon which my Lord has mercy."* (Qur'an 12:53)

The barrier between the two seas within is not permanent and not self-maintaining. Verse 55:20 says the two seas *do not transgress* the barrier — *lā yabghiyān* — and the verb *baghā* means to transgress, to exceed, to aggress. The allegory's point is that the soul's two seas are held apart by a divine constitution, and that insolence toward God is what breaches it.

**Bal Aktharuhum Lā Yaʿlamūn: The Second Diagnosis**

*"Nay, but most of them know not."* The refrain's diagnosis changes across the five verses, and the change is significant. Verse 60 ended with *they set up equals* — an act. Verse 61 ends with *most of them do not know* — a state of ignorance. Verse 62 will end with *little do you reflect* — a failure of thought. Verse 63 with *exalted is God above what they associate* — a declaration of transcendence. Verse 64 with *bring your proof, if you are truthful* — a demand for evidence.

The five diagnoses are progressive and they cover the full range of the failure. They act (they set up equals); they do not know; they do not reflect; they exceed God's transcendence; they have no proof. And *aktharuhum* — "most of them" — introduces a qualification the earlier verses did not have: not all, but most. Some know. The knowing minority is the audience the five questions are actually addressed to, and the sūrah has already described them at verse 3.

*Yaʿlamūn* is from *ʿ-l-m*, knowledge — the verb of verse 6, where the Qur'an is received from *One Wise, Knowing* (*ḥakīmin ʿalīm*). The sūrah's interest in knowledge runs from its opening to its closing: verse 15 gave knowledge to David and Solomon; verse 40 transported a throne by knowledge of the Book; verse 42 said *"we were given knowledge before her"*; and verse 61 says most of them do not know. Knowledge in al-Naml is not information; it is the capacity to read the created order as evidence, and the verse's diagnosis is that most people lack it while surrounded by the evidence.

**The Verse's Application: Four Facts About the Ground You Are Standing On**

Verse 61 asks the reader to consider four things, and all four are available without leaving the room.

The ground is *stable*. It does not move under you, does not buckle, does not shift while you sleep. This is so constant a feature of experience that it has become invisible — which is precisely what the verse is correcting. Stability is not the default condition of a planetary surface; it is a constituted one, and the verse names its constitution as an act of making.

Water moves *through* it. Not on the surface only but *khilālahā* — in its midst, in its interior spaces. Aquifers, springs, rivers, wells: the water is distributed inside the ground, and the distribution is what makes inland habitation possible. Every city not on a coast exists because of this clause.

Mountains are *fixed* in it. The most immobile features of the visible world, and the verse describes them as *rawāsī* — anchored, moored, made to stay. The image is nautical: the mountains are the earth's anchors.

Two seas are kept *apart*. Salt and fresh, meeting and not mixing, at every estuary and every river mouth on the planet. The phenomenon is local enough that any person who has stood where a river enters the sea has stood at the barrier the verse describes.

Four facts. None of them required belief to observe. None of them is produced by anything the observer can name as a partner. And the refrain waits at the end of the list: *is there a god alongside God?* Then the diagnosis: *nay, but most of them know not* — and the implication that the reader has just ceased to be among the most.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:62

> **He, Who answers the one in distress when he calls upon Him and removes the evil, and Who makes you vicegerents of the earth? Is there a god alongside God? Little do you reflect!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Question: The One Place Everybody Knows**

*Amman yujību'l-muḍṭarra idhā daʿāhu wa yakshifu'l-sūʾa wa yajʿalukum khulafāʾa'l-arḍ a-ilāhun maʿa Llāhi qalīlan mā tadhakkarūn* — "Or is He who answers the one in distress when he calls upon Him, and removes the harm, and makes you successors in the earth? Is there a god alongside God? Little do you take heed!"

Verses 60 and 61 argued from the external world — sky, water, gardens, mountains, seas. Verse 62 turns inward, to an experience every human being has had and none can deny. The argument shifts from cosmology to biography, and it is the most powerful of the five because it does not require the hearer to look at anything. It requires him to remember.

**Al-Muḍṭarr: The Word for the Person With No Options Left**

*Al-muḍṭarr* is the passive participle of Form VIII from *ḍ-r-r*, harm, distress, necessity — the same root as *ḍurr*, harm, *ḍarar*, damage, and *iḍṭirār*, a state of compulsion or dire necessity. The *muḍṭarr* is one who has been *driven into* distress: not merely suffering but compelled, cornered, brought to the point where his own resources are exhausted.

Al-Qurṭubī explains the word as referring to one who is helpless and powerless; al-Rāzī adds that it denotes a person in a bad situation through no choice of his own, perhaps afflicted by poverty or sickness. The defining feature of the *muḍṭarr* is the exhaustion of options. He has tried everything available to him, and everything has failed, and there is nothing left to do but call.

The Qur'an uses the same root in the parallel statement of this verse's argument:

> *"Is He not the One Who answers the distressed (al-muḍṭarr) when he calls upon Him, and removes the evil, and makes you successors in the earth? Is there a god alongside God? Little do you take heed!"* (Qur'an 27:62)

> *"And when adversity touches man, he calls upon Us, whether lying on his side, or sitting, or standing; but when We remove his adversity from him, he passes on as though he had never called upon Us for an adversity that touched him."* (Qur'an 10:12)

> *"And when adversity touches man, he calls upon his Lord, turning unto Him; then when He confers upon him a favor from Him, he forgets what he used to call upon before, and he sets up rivals to God, to lead astray from His way. Say: 'Enjoy thy disbelief a little; verily thou art among the companions of the Fire.'"* (Qur'an 39:8)

> *"And when harm touches man, he calls upon Us; then when We confer upon him a favor from Us, he says: 'I was given it only by knowledge.' Nay, but it is a trial — yet most of them know not."* (Qur'an 39:49)

Verse 10:12 is the Qur'an's most exact description of the *muḍṭarr*, and it adds the detail that makes the argument work: he calls *in every posture* — lying, sitting, standing. The call is not scheduled, not ritualized, not conditional on purity or preparation. It comes from the body's exhaustion, and it is addressed to God by people who may not otherwise acknowledge Him.

**Yujību: The Verb of Answering**

*"Who answers."* *Ajāba* is Form IV from *j-w-b*, to answer, to respond — the same root as *jawāb* at verse 56, where Lot's people's "answer" was nothing but an expulsion order. The Qur'an uses the verb of God's answering, and it makes the answering a promise:

> *"And your Lord said: 'Call upon Me; I will answer you (astajib lakum). Verily those who are too proud to worship Me shall enter Hell, humbled.'"* (Qur'an 40:60)

> *"And when My servants ask thee concerning Me — verily I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me. So let them respond to Me, and let them believe in Me, that haply they may be rightly guided."* (Qur'an 2:186)

> *"And He answers those who believe and work righteousness, and increases them of His bounty."* (Qur'an 42:26)

Verse 2:186 is the Qur'an's most intimate statement of the relation, and its placement — in the middle of the verses on fasting, at the point where the fasting person is at his weakest and most attentive — is deliberate. God describes Himself as *qarīb*, near, and the nearness is defined operationally: *I answer the call of the caller when he calls.*

**Idhā Daʿāhu: "When He Calls Upon Him"**

The condition attached to the answer is a single clause: *when he calls upon Him*. The condition is minimal, and its minimality is the argument. The *muḍṭarr* is not required to be righteous, to be a believer, to be in a state of purity, to be facing the qiblah, to have performed ablution, or to know God's names. He is required to call. And the verse asserts that when he does, the call is answered.

The Qur'an's own examples are drawn from people who were not believers at the time of the call:

> *"And when they board a ship, they call upon God, sincere in faith to Him; but when He brings them safe to land, behold, they associate."* (Qur'an 29:65)

> *"It is He Who carries you on the land and the sea, until, when you are in ships and they run with them in a fair wind, and they rejoice therein — a storm wind comes upon them, and the waves come upon them from every side, and they suppose that they are encompassed; then they call upon God, sincere in faith to Him: 'If Thou deliver us from this, we shall surely be among the grateful.' But when He delivers them, behold, they transgress in the earth without right."* (Qur'an 10:22–23)

Verse 29:65 is the decisive text for understanding verse 62. The people described call upon God *sincerely* — *mukhliṣīna lahu'l-dīn* — in the storm, and associate partners with Him on the shore. The sincerity in the storm is real; the *muḍṭarr* is, in the moment of his distress, a monotheist. Verse 62's argument is built on this universal experience: at the point of exhaustion, every human being knows exactly whom to call, and the knowledge is not learned. It is recovered.

**Wa Yakshifu'l-Sūʾ: The Removal of the Harm**

*"And removes the harm."* *Kashafa* is from *k-sh-f*, to uncover, to remove a covering, to lift — the same verb used at verse 44 of Bilqīs baring her legs (*kashafat ʿan sāqayhā*). The Qur'an uses it of God's removal of affliction:

> *"And if God touches thee with harm, none can remove it but He; and if He touches thee with good, He is over all things Powerful."* (Qur'an 6:17)

> *"Or is He who guides you in the darknesses of the land and the sea...?"* — cf. 10:107

> *"And We removed (fa-kashafnā) from them the punishment; behold, they broke [their word]."* — cf. 44:15

> *"And Job, when he called upon his Lord: 'Verily adversity has touched me, and Thou art the Most Merciful of the merciful.' So We answered him and removed (fa-kashafnā) what was with him of adversity, and We gave him his family and the like thereof with them, as a mercy from Us and a reminder for those who worship."* (Qur'an 21:83–84)

*Sūʾ* is the word used at verse 5 — *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb*, "a terrible punishment" — and at verse 12 of the white hand *min ghayri sūʾ*, "without blemish." The root *s-w-ʾ* denotes what is bad, evil, harmful, displeasing. Its use here is comprehensive: God removes *the* harm, whatever form it takes — illness, poverty, fear, loss, danger.

**Wa Yajʿalukum Khulafāʾa'l-Arḍ: Successors in the Earth**

*"And makes you successors in the earth."* *Khulafāʾ* is the plural of *khalīfah* — from *kh-l-f*, to succeed, to follow after, to replace, to act in another's stead. The Qur'an uses the word of the human being's appointed position in creation, and it is one of the most consequential terms in Islamic anthropology:

> *"And [remember] when thy Lord said unto the angels: 'Verily I am placing in the earth a vicegerent (khālifah).'"* (Qur'an 2:30)

> *"And it is He Who has made you successors (khalāʾif) in the earth, and has raised some of you above others in degrees, that He may test you in what He has given you."* (Qur'an 6:165)

> *"And [remember] when He made you successors after the people of Noah, and increased you in stature abundantly."* (Qur'an 7:69)

> *"Then We made you successors in the earth after them, that We might see how you would act."* (Qur'an 10:14)

> *"O David, verily We have made thee a viceroy (khālifah) in the earth; so judge between people with truth."* (Qur'an 38:26)

The concept of *istikhlāf* — being made a successor, a deputy, a steward — carries a specific theological content: the human being holds the earth not as owner but as *deputy*, and a deputy is accountable to the one he deputizes. Verse 6:165 states the consequence directly: the elevation into degrees is *for the purpose of testing*, and 10:14 says the same — *"that We might see how you would act."*

Verse 62 places this enormous concept inside a list of God's answering acts, and its placement makes an argument. The God who answers the distressed is also the God who appointed the one He answered as His deputy on earth. The two facts belong together: the person in distress is not a supplicant to a distant power but a *khalīfah* calling on the One who installed him.

**Qalīlan Mā Tadhakkarūn: The Third Diagnosis**

*"Little do you take heed!"* The phrase *qalīlan mā tadhakkarūn* is an idiomatic Arabic construction of emphatic negation: *qalīl* ("little") followed by *mā*, which in this construction intensifies rather than negates — the sense is "how little you take heed," "very little indeed do you remember."

*Tadhakkarūn* is from *dh-k-r*, to remember, to recall, to mention, to take heed — the same root as *dhikr*, remembrance, and *tadhkirah*, reminder. The Qur'an uses the root constantly of its own function:

> *"And remind; verily the reminder profits the believers."* (Qur'an 51:55)

> *"So remind — thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a taskmaster over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

> *"And this is a blessed Reminder (dhikr) that We have sent down. Will you then be deniers of it?"* (Qur'an 21:50)

The diagnosis is precise and it differs from verse 61's. Verse 61 said *most of them know not* — a lack of knowledge. Verse 62 says *little do you remember* — a failure of recall. The two are different failures. Not knowing is an absence; not remembering is a presence unaccessed. The *muḍṭarr* experience is in every person's memory — the storm, the illness, the night of fear, the moment when all options ran out and the call went up — and verse 62's accusation is not that the reader lacks the data but that he does not consult it.

The Qur'an states the mechanism of forgetting explicitly, and it is the mechanism of verse 10:12: when the distress is removed, the person *passes on as though he had never called.* The removal of the harm produces the forgetting of the One who removed it. This is why *dhikr* — remembrance — is legislated in Islam as a fixed daily practice rather than left to feeling: the practice exists because the forgetting is structural and predictable.

**Al-Qurṭubī's Ḥadīth on the Oppressed**

Al-Qurṭubī, as the source commentary records, quotes at this verse a ḥadīth connecting *al-muḍṭarr* to those who are wronged or oppressed:

> *"Be mindful of the supplication of the oppressed (daʿwat al-maẓlūm), for there is no veil between it and God."* (al-Bukhārī 1496; Muslim 19 — from Ibn ʿAbbās, in the longer narration of Muʿādh's dispatch to Yemen)

The full report is one of the most important in Islamic political ethics. The Prophet ﷺ sent Muʿādh to Yemen and instructed him:

> *"Thou art going to a people of the Book. Let the first thing thou callest them to be the testimony that there is no god but God... If they obey thee in that, inform them that God has enjoined upon them five prayers in every day and night... If they obey thee in that, inform them that God has enjoined upon them charity, taken from their rich and given to their poor. If they obey thee in that, beware of the choicest of their wealth; and fear the supplication of the oppressed, for there is no veil between it and God."* (al-Bukhārī 1395; Muslim 19)

The connection to verse 62 is exact. The oppressed person is the paradigm *muḍṭarr*: he has exhausted his options, his resources have been taken, his appeals have failed, and the only instrument left to him is the call. And the Prophet ﷺ states that this call has *no veil* — no barrier, no intermediary, no delay — between it and God.

The legal and political consequence drawn by the tradition is severe for those in power. A governor, an employer, a judge, a parent, or a spouse who creates a *muḍṭarr* has created an unblocked channel to God, and the channel carries the one thing that person has left. The verse and the ḥadīth together are the Islamic tradition's most direct statement about the metaphysics of injustice: the oppressed are not weak. They are *connected*.

**The Sufi Readings: The Distress That Is Not Worldly**

The source commentary records two further readings from the Sufi tradition. For some, *al-muḍṭarr* refers to those who have cut themselves off from attachment to everything but God and see no answer to their distress except God. Others say the phrase refers to those who raise their hands in prayer to God but have not a single good deed to present — such people have only their past sins to show, and cannot approach God with an attitude of merit; yet their prayers are answered by God's pure generosity, meaning that those who come with awareness of their complete helplessness before God will find their prayers answered no matter what their actions may have been.

The second reading is theologically precise and it explains a difficulty every believer encounters: the person with a record of sin who feels he cannot pray. The reading's answer is that the *absence* of merit is not an obstacle to the answering — it is the qualification. The verse is about the *muḍṭarr*, and the *muḍṭarr* is by definition one who has nothing. A person who presents himself with deeds presents himself as a creditor; a person who presents himself with nothing presents himself as a *muḍṭarr*, and verse 62 promises the answer to the second.

The Prophet ﷺ's own formulation of the principle is in the duʿāʾ recorded by al-Tirmidhī and al-Ḥākim, the duʿāʾ of Dhū'l-Nūn:

> *"The supplication of Dhū'l-Nūn, when he supplicated in the belly of the whale: 'There is no god but Thou! Glory be to Thee! Verily I was among the wrongdoers' — no Muslim supplicates with it for anything but that God answers him."* (al-Tirmidhī 3505, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

Jonah in the whale is the Qur'an's paradigm *muḍṭarr* — three darknesses (21:87: the darkness of the night, the sea, and the belly), no options, no resources, no merit — and his call is the one the Prophet ﷺ guarantees will be answered. Verse 62 is the doctrine; Jonah is the case study; and Bilqīs at verse 44, saying *"My Lord, truly I have wronged myself,"* is the same prayer in a glass pavilion.

**The Verse's Application: The Memory Everybody Has**

Verse 62 requires nothing from the reader except honesty about his own history. Every person alive has been a *muḍṭarr*. There has been a night — an illness, a diagnosis, a debt, a flight, a bereavement, a moment in a vehicle, a phone call awaited — in which every option was exhausted and something in you called out, in words or without them, to a Someone.

The verse makes three claims about that night, and they can be tested against memory.

First: you called. Not a concept, not a principle, not "the universe." The call had a direction, and the direction was upward and outward, toward something you did not then and may not now describe theologically. Verse 29:65 names the quality of that call: *sincere*.

Second: the harm was removed. You are here reading. The illness broke, the debt was met, the plane landed, the news was better than feared, or the worst happened and you survived it. Verse 62 says the removal was an answering, and it attributes the answering to the One the verse has been describing since verse 60.

Third: you forgot. Verse 62's diagnosis is *qalīlan mā tadhakkarūn* — how little you remember — and the Qur'an's own account of the mechanism (10:12: *"he passes on as though he had never called"*) is confirmed by anyone's honest recollection of how quickly the night recedes once the morning comes.

The refrain then asks its question of a reader who has just been walked through his own biography: *is there a god alongside God?* The reader who remembers his night cannot answer yes, because in the night there was only One. And that is the entire argument: not that God exists — the storm already established that — but that the person who knows it in his extremity has failed to know it in his comfort.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:63

> **He, Who guides you in the darkness of land and sea, and sends the winds as glad tidings ahead of His Mercy? Is there a god alongside God? Exalted is God above the partners they ascribe!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Question: Navigation and Weather**

*Amman yahdīkum fī ẓulumāti'l-barri wa'l-baḥri wa man yursilu'l-riyāḥa bushran bayna yaday raḥmatihī a-ilāhun maʿa Llāhi taʿālā Llāhu ʿammā yushrikūn* — "Or is He who guides you in the darknesses of the land and the sea, and who sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy? Is there a god alongside God? Exalted is God above what they associate!"

The verse presents two divine acts: guidance through darkness, and the sending of winds as heralds of rain. The first concerns the traveler's dependence; the second concerns the farmer's. Between them they cover the two great uncertainties of pre-modern life — where you are, and whether it will rain.

**Yahdīkum: The Verb of Guidance Applied to Travel**

*"Who guides you."* The verb is *h-d-y*, discussed at verse 2 — to lead gently along a road, to show the way. Here it is used in its most literal, physical sense: to guide a traveler who cannot see the road.

The Qur'an uses this physical guidance as the basis of a repeated argument, and the parallel passage in al-Anʿām is nearly identical:

> *"And He it is Who made the stars for you, that you may be guided by them in the darknesses of the land and the sea. Verily We have detailed the signs for a people who know."* (Qur'an 6:97)

> *"And it is He Who set for you the stars, that you may be guided by them in the darknesses of the land and the sea."* — and: *"And signs; and by the star they are guided."* (Qur'an 16:16)

> *"And We made the sky a guarded canopy, yet they turn away from its signs."* — cf. 21:32

> *"And He cast into the earth firmly set mountains lest it shake with you, and rivers and paths, that haply you might be guided, and landmarks; and by the star they are guided."* (Qur'an 16:15–16)

The mechanism the Qur'an names is astronomical: the stars are the guidance, and they are guidance *made* — *jaʿala*, the same verb of constitution used four times in verse 61. The stars are not accidentally positioned; they were appointed to the function of orientation.

**Ẓulumāti'l-Barri Wa'l-Baḥr: The Darknesses Plural**

*"In the darknesses of the land and the sea."* *Ẓulumāt* is the plural of *ẓulmah*, darkness — and the Qur'an consistently uses the plural for darkness and the singular for light:

> *"God is the Protector of those who believe; He brings them out of the darknesses (ẓulumāt) into the light (nūr)."* (Qur'an 2:257)

> *"Praise be to God, Who created the heavens and the earth and made the darknesses (ẓulumāt) and the light (nūr)."* (Qur'an 6:1)

> *"And [He made] the moon a light therein and made the sun a lamp."* (Qur'an 71:16)

The commentators note the plural's significance: darkness is multiple and layered — the darkness of night, of cloud, of terrain, of fog, of the interior of a valley, of the open sea with no landmark. Each darkness requires a different guidance, and the plural indicates that God's guidance addresses them all. Light, by contrast, is one: there is one way to see.

Al-Qurṭubī explains the darkness of land and sea as covering both the literal sense of physical danger — a traveler at night in unknown country, a ship in a night sea — and a wilderness without guideposts or signs to show the way. The verse is thus about *disorientation* in its most concrete form: the state of not knowing where you are and not being able to find out.

**The Sea in the Qur'an: The Paradigm Case of Dependency**

The Qur'an returns to the sea constantly as the setting in which human beings discover their dependence, and the passages are among its most psychologically acute:

> *"And when they board a ship, they call upon God, sincere in faith to Him; but when He brings them safe to land, behold, they associate partners."* (Qur'an 29:65)

> *"It is He Who carries you in the land and the sea, until, when you are in ships and they run with them in a fair wind, and they rejoice therein — a storm wind comes upon them, and the waves come upon them from every side, and they suppose that they are encompassed; then they call upon God, sincere in faith to Him."* (Qur'an 10:22)

> *"And among His signs are the ships like mountains in the sea."* (Qur'an 42:32)

> *"His are the ships raised high in the sea like landmarks."* (Qur'an 55:24)

> *"Or who is it that can be an army for you to help you apart from the Compassionate?"* — and the sea passage at 17:67: *"And when adversity touches you at sea, those you call upon apart from Him are lost to you; then when He delivers you to the land, you turn away. And man is ever ungrateful."*

Verse 17:67 is the most precise statement of what verse 63 asserts: at sea, in adversity, the false gods are *lost* — *ḍalla ʿankum mā tadʿūna*, literally "that which you used to call upon is astray from you" — and only God remains callable. The sea functions in the Qur'an as a laboratory in which the human being's actual theology is exposed, because the sea removes everything else.

**Wa Man Yursilu'l-Riyāḥa Bushran: The Winds as Heralds**

*"And who sends the winds as glad tidings."* The source commentary notes that the winds are described as *bushr* — good news, glad tidings — the same word discussed at verse 2 (*hudan wa bushrā*, "a guidance and glad tidings"). The application to winds is exact and meteorological: the wind that gathers the cloud before the rain arrives is the herald of the rain, and a farmer in a dry land knows the sign as certainly as a reader knows good news.

The parallel passages give the full description:

> *"And He it is Who sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy (bushran bayna yaday raḥmatih), until, when they have carried a heavy cloud, We drive it unto a dead land, and We send down water therewith, and We bring forth therewith every kind of fruit."* (Qur'an 7:57)

> *"And it is He Who sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy, and We send down pure water from the sky."* (Qur'an 25:48)

> *"And among His signs is that He sends the winds as bringers of good news (mubashshirāt), that He may let you taste of His mercy, and that the ships may run at His command, and that you may seek of His bounty, and that haply you may give thanks."* (Qur'an 30:46)

Verse 30:46 is the fullest version, and it lists the three functions of the winds: they announce the mercy, they fill the sails, and they produce gratitude. The winds in the Qur'an are never merely weather; they are a communication system, and the communication is good news.

The source commentary directs the reader to 7:57 and 25:48 for the winds as signs and blessings, and notes further mentions at 2:164, 15:22, and 45:5:

> *"Verily in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the alternation of the night and the day, and the ship that runs in the sea with what profits mankind, and the water God sends down from the sky whereby He revives the earth after its death and scatters therein every kind of creature, and the turning of the winds, and the cloud subjected between the sky and the earth — verily in that are signs for a people who use their reason."* (Qur'an 2:164)

Verse 2:164 is the Qur'an's own catalogue of the phenomena listed across verses 60–63, presented in a single verse and addressed to *a people who use their reason*. Al-Naml's five questions are that catalogue expanded into an interrogation.

**Bayna Yaday Raḥmatih: "Before His Mercy"**

*"Before His mercy."* The idiom *bayna yaday* — literally "between the two hands of" — means immediately before, in advance of, preceding. The mercy is the rain, and the Qur'an names it as mercy explicitly:

> *"And He it is Who sends down the rain (ghayth) after they have despaired, and spreads His mercy (yan-shuru raḥmatahu)."* (Qur'an 42:28)

> *"And of His mercy He made for you the night and the day, that you may rest therein and seek of His bounty."* (Qur'an 28:73)

The naming of rain as *raḥmah* is one of the Qur'an's most consistent usages, and it shapes the entire vocabulary of mercy in Islam. *Raḥmah* — from the womb, as discussed at verse 30 — is applied to rain because rain is what sustains life from above, without being earned, in a manner the recipient cannot control. The wind announces it; the cloud carries it; the land receives it; and nothing in the sequence is produced by the one who needs it.

**Taʿālā Llāhu ʿAmmā Yushrikūn: The Fourth Diagnosis as a Declaration**

*"Exalted is God above what they associate!"* The refrain's diagnosis changes form for the fourth time. Verse 60 diagnosed an act (*they set up equals*); verse 61 a state (*most of them know not*); verse 62 a failure of memory (*little do you take heed*); verse 63 makes a declaration of transcendence.

*Taʿālā* is Form VI (or, on some analyses, Form V with a long vowel) from *ʿ-l-w*, height, elevation — the same root as *ʿuluwwan* at verse 14 and *al-ʿAzīz* at verse 9. Form VI, *taʿālā*, means "He is exalted, He is high, He transcends," and it is used exclusively of God in the Qur'an:

> *"Exalted is He above what they associate."* (Qur'an 6:100; 10:18; 16:1; 20:40; 23:91; 28:68; 30:40; 39:67; 52:43; 59:23)

> *"Exalted is God, the King, the Real. And hasten not with the Qur'an before its revelation is completed unto thee, and say: 'My Lord, increase me in knowledge.'"* (Qur'an 20:114)

The declaration is placed where it is because verse 63 has described two acts — guidance in darkness and the sending of winds — that no associated object can perform, and the natural human response to the demonstration is not an argument but an exclamation. The Qur'an puts the exclamation in the text.

Al-Zamakhsharī notes that *taʿālā* is followed here by *ʿammā yushrikūn* — "above *what* they associate" — using *mā*, the relative for non-rational referents, as at verse 59. The associated objects are *things*, and God is exalted above things. The declaration is thus not a refutation of a rival theology but a refusal of the category.

**The Verse's Application: The Two Uncertainties That Remain**

Verse 63 addresses the two uncertainties that governed pre-modern life — location and weather — and a modern reader may suppose that technology has dissolved both. GPS has replaced the stars; meteorology has replaced the reading of winds. The verse's argument, however, does not depend on the absence of technology. It depends on the presence of *guidance* and of *good news*, and both remain exactly where they were.

The GPS system works because a constellation of satellites maintains atomic-clock synchronization and broadcasts positions; the receiver computes a fix from signals it did not send and cannot influence. The user is still being *guided in the darkness* — the darkness is now informational rather than nocturnal — and the guidance still comes from a system he did not make, does not control, and could not operate. What has changed is the intermediary, not the dependence. Verse 6:97 named the intermediary of its own age: *the stars, that you may be guided by them*. The verse's argument is about the appointment, not the instrument.

Meteorology works because the atmosphere behaves lawfully and the behavior can be measured; the forecast announces the rain before it falls, exactly as the winds did. Verse 30:46 describes the winds as *mubashshirāt* — bringers of good news — and a modern forecast is a *bushrā* in the same structural position: an announcement, in advance, of something the hearer needs and cannot produce. The farmer who checks an app before planting is doing what the Arabian farmer did by reading the wind; he is receiving good news from a system he did not author.

The verse's question therefore stands unchanged: *who guides you in the darkness, and who sends the good news before the mercy?* The technologies answer *how*, and they answer it impressively. They do not answer *who appointed the laws the technologies exploit*, and verse 63 is asking the second question.

And the declaration that closes the verse is the correct response to the answer. *Taʿālā Llāhu ʿammā yushrikūn* — exalted is God above what they associate. In an age whose associated objects are not stones but systems, platforms, algorithms, markets, and metrics, the declaration is as necessary as it was in an age of idols, and it is made in the same direction: above all of them.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:64

> **He, Who brings creation into being, then brings it back, and Who provides for you from Heaven and the earth? Is there a god alongside God? Say, "Bring your proof, if you are truthful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth Question, and the Demand for Evidence**

*Amman yabdaʾu'l-khalqa thumma yuʿīduhu wa man yarzuqukum mina'l-samāʾi wa'l-arḍi a-ilāhun maʿa Llāhi qul hātū burhānakum in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — "Or is He who originates creation, then brings it back, and who provides for you from the heaven and the earth? Is there a god alongside God? Say: 'Bring your proof, if you are truthful.'"

The fifth question completes the series, and it is the only one that ends not with a diagnosis of the hearers but with a demand addressed to them. The four previous verses concluded: they set up equals; most of them know not; little do you take heed; exalted is God above what they associate. The fifth concludes: *bring your proof.*

**Yabdaʾu'l-Khalqa Thumma Yuʿīduhu: Origination and Return**

*"Who originates creation, then brings it back."* Two verbs from two roots, and the pair states the Qur'an's complete cosmology in four words.

*Yabdaʾu* is from *b-d-ʾ*, to begin, to originate, to commence — the same root as *badʾ*, beginning, *ibdāʾ*, origination, and *badīʿ*, originator without precedent. *Al-Badīʿ* is a Divine Name:

> *"The Originator (Badīʿ) of the heavens and the earth; and when He decrees a matter, He says unto it only 'Be,' and it is."* (Qur'an 2:117)

> *"Originator of the heavens and the earth. How should He have a son when He has no consort, and He created all things, and He knows all things?"* (Qur'an 6:101)

*Yuʿīduhu* is Form IV from *ʿ-w-d*, to return, to repeat, to do again — the same root as *ʿawdah*, return, *maʿād*, the place of return, and *muʿīd*, restorer. The Qur'an uses the verb of resurrection consistently:

> *"Verily He originates and returns (yuʾbdiʾu wa yuʿīd)."* — cf. 85:13

> *"God originates creation, then He brings it back (yuʿīduhu), then unto Him you shall be returned."* (Qur'an 30:11)

> *"And it is He Who originates creation, then brings it back, and that is easier for Him."* (Qur'an 30:27)

> *"Say: 'Is there any of your partners who originates creation, then brings it back?' Say: 'God originates creation, then brings it back; so how are you turned away?'"* (Qur'an 10:34)

Verse 10:34 is the direct parallel and it supplies the argument verse 64 assumes. The Qur'an's claim is that neither origination nor return is available to any associated object, and that the two acts together define divinity. Al-Rāzī, as the source commentary notes, explains that origination and return encompass all the other blessings God provides from heaven and earth; and that the message of the prophets is not only that God begins everything — which even many idolaters accepted — but that all things will return to Him. The Origin indicates and points to the Return.

**The Argument From Origination to Return**

The Qur'an's argument for resurrection rests substantially on the argument from origination, and it states the logic explicitly:

> *"And he strikes a likeness for Us and forgets his own creation. He says: 'Who will give life to bones when they are rotten?' Say: 'He will give them life Who originated them the first time; and He is Knower of every creation.'"* (Qur'an 36:78–79)

> *"Is not He Who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like thereof? Yea, indeed, and He is the Knowing Creator."* (Qur'an 36:81 — cited by al-Rāzī at verse 67 of this sūrah)

> *"And indeed the first creation was not beyond Us; nay, but they are in doubt about a new creation."* (Qur'an 50:15)

> *"Say: 'He will revive them Who produced them the first time; and He is Knower of every creation.'"* (Qur'an 36:79)

> *"And We have certainly created man from an extract of clay... Then We produced it as another creation. So blessed be God, the Best of Creators."* (Qur'an 23:12–14)

The logic is a simple inference from the harder to the easier, and verse 30:27 states it in those terms: *"and that is easier for Him"* — re-creation is easier than origination, by the ordinary reasoning of any craftsman, and God's case is stronger still since He is not a craftsman working on resistant material.

Verse 50:15 makes the point epistemically: the first creation was not beyond Us — you are here, you exist, you were produced — so why is the second in doubt? The doubt is not about capacity; it is about *willingness*, and the willingness is stated as a promise.

**Wa Man Yarzuqukum Mina'l-Samāʾi Wa'l-Arḍ: Provision From Both Directions**

*"And who provides for you from the heaven and the earth."* *Yarzuqukum* is from *r-z-q*, provision, sustenance — the same root as *rizq*, provision, and *rāziq*, provider. *Al-Razzāq* is a Divine Name:

> *"Verily God is the Provider (al-Razzāq), the Possessor of Strength, the Firm."* (Qur'an 51:58)

> *"And there is no creature on earth but that its provision (rizquhā) is upon God; and He knows its dwelling place and its place of deposit. All is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 11:6)

Verse 11:6 is the Qur'an's most comprehensive statement on provision, and it connects directly to verse 75 of this sūrah — *"And there is nothing hidden in heaven or earth but that it is in a clear Book"* — using the identical formula of comprehensive knowledge recorded in a Book.

*Mina'l-samāʾ wa'l-arḍ* — "from the heaven and the earth" — specifies the two directions from which provision arrives, and the specification is exhaustive: rain from above, produce from below. The source commentary records that for some Sufis the provisions from heaven and earth refer to spiritual and material blessings respectively, and the reading is coherent with the Qur'an's usage:

> *"And had they established the Torah and the Gospel and what was sent down unto them from their Lord, they would have eaten from above them and from beneath their feet."* (Qur'an 5:66)

> *"And We opened upon them the gates of every thing, until, when they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair."* (Qur'an 6:44)

Verse 5:66 uses the same spatial formula — from above and from beneath the feet — and connects provision directly to the establishment of revelation.

**Qul Hātū Burhānakum: The Demand for Proof**

*"Say: Bring your proof."* The section's five questions end with a command to demand evidence, and the demand is stated in the strongest available vocabulary.

*Hātū* is the imperative plural of a defective verb (*hāta / yaʾtī*) used in Arabic exclusively in the imperative and the active participle, meaning "bring here, produce, hand over." The word carries a sense of immediacy: produce it *now*, bring it *here*. The Qur'an uses it in challenges:

> *"Say: 'Bring your proof, if you are truthful.'"* (Qur'an 27:64; 2:111; 21:24)

> *"Say: 'Bring your witnesses, who testify that God has forbidden this.'"* — cf. 6:150

*Burhān* is from *b-r-h-n*, proof, demonstration, decisive evidence — a loanword in Arabic, according to the lexicographers, and used in the Qur'an for the strongest class of evidence:
