> *"O mankind! There has come unto you a burhān from your Lord, and We have sent down unto you a clear light."* (Qur'an 4:174)

> *"And Moses said to Pharaoh: 'Verily thou knowest that none sent down these [signs] save the Lord of the heavens and the earth, as proofs (baṣāʾir).'"* — cf. 17:102

> *"Those who dispute concerning the signs of God, without any authority (sulṭān) that has come unto them — great is the hatred [of it] with God and with those who believe. Thus does God seal upon every arrogant, tyrannical heart."* (Qur'an 40:35)

Verse 2:111 uses the same demand in a religious dispute:

> *"And they say: 'None shall enter the Garden save he who is a Jew or a Christian.' Those are their wishful thoughts. Say: 'Bring your proof (burhānakum), if you are truthful.'"*

Verse 21:24 uses it of the gods themselves:

> *"Or have they taken gods apart from Him? Say: 'Bring your proof (burhānakum). This is a Reminder for those with me and a Reminder for those before me.' Nay, but most of them know not the truth, so they turn away."*

The demand is the Qur'an's standing challenge, and its form is instructive: the burden of proof is placed on the party making the positive claim. The claim being challenged in verse 64 is that there are gods alongside God — a positive existential claim, requiring evidence. The Qur'an does not ask the disbeliever to disprove God; it asks the associator to produce his *burhān* for the association.

**In Kuntum Ṣādiqīn: The Condition**

*"If you are truthful."* The conditional clause uses *ṣadaqa*, to be truthful — the same verb Solomon used of the hoopoe at verse 27 (*a-ṣadaqta am kunta mina'l-kādhibīn*, "did you speak truth, or are you among the liars?"). The sūrah thus closes its argumentative section with the same vocabulary it used at the start of its narrative verification, and the parallel is exact: a claim has been made, and evidence is required.

The condition *in kuntum ṣādiqīn* is a challenge to the claimants' self-characterization. They present themselves as truthful people following the religion of their fathers; the Qur'an asks them to substantiate the presentation. The same formula appears throughout:

> *"Say: 'Do you have any knowledge that you can bring out to us? You follow nothing but conjecture, and you do nothing but lie.'"* (Qur'an 6:148)

> *"Nay, but they follow conjecture, and conjecture avails nothing against the truth."* (Qur'an 53:28)

> *"And most of them follow nothing but conjecture. Verily conjecture avails nothing against the truth. Verily God is Knower of what they do."* (Qur'an 10:36)

The Qur'an's characterization of the opposing position is thus precise: it is *ẓann*, conjecture — a supposition without demonstration. And the demand for *burhān* is the demand that distinguishes the two.

**The Five Questions as a Complete System of Evidence**

The section from verse 59 to verse 64 constitutes the Qur'an's most concentrated natural-theological argument, and its five questions can be set out as a system.

**Verse 60** argues from *cosmic creation and vegetable growth*: the heavens and the earth were created, water was sent down, gardens grew, and you cannot cause a tree to grow. Domain: the sky and the biosphere. Diagnosis: they set up equals.

**Verse 61** argues from *terrestrial habitability*: the earth is a settled abode, rivers run through it, mountains are fixed in it, and two seas are held apart. Domain: the geosphere and hydrosphere. Diagnosis: most of them know not.

**Verse 62** argues from *answered distress and human succession*: the distressed one calls and is answered, harm is removed, and human beings are made successors in the earth. Domain: the interior life and history. Diagnosis: little do you take heed.

**Verse 63** argues from *navigation and weather*: God guides in the darknesses of land and sea, and sends the winds as good news before the rain. Domain: human movement and agriculture. Diagnosis: exalted is God above what they associate.

**Verse 64** argues from *origination, return, and provision*: creation is begun and will be brought back, and provision arrives from the sky and the earth. Domain: the whole of existence, from beginning to end. Diagnosis: bring your proof.

The five domains move from the external to the internal and from the spatial to the temporal: sky and earth, ground and water, distress and succession, travel and weather, beginning and end. And every one of them is an experience available to the hearer without expertise. The argument is not technical; it is *comprehensive*, and its comprehensiveness is its force — there is no domain of ordinary experience left in which an associated object could be located.

**The Refrain's Cumulative Effect**

The source commentary notes that the refrain *a-ilāhun maʿa Llāh* punctuates the passage several times, and that no other sūrah repeats a sentence so insistently within so few verses. The effect on a hearer is worth describing precisely, because it is rhetorical rather than logical.

The first occurrence (v. 60) invites consideration. The second (v. 61) establishes a pattern. The third (v. 62) makes the hearer aware that he is being led somewhere. The fourth (v. 63) produces expectation. The fifth (v. 64) arrives with the weight of the four behind it, and the demand for proof that follows it is heard not as a challenge to someone else but as a question about oneself: *what is your proof?*

The refrain never receives an answer in the sūrah, and its silence is the answer. Al-Rāzī's observation about the hanging comparison at verse 60 applies to the whole section: the questions are structured so that God's power and beneficence appear so great that one should not even offer an explicit comparison with Him. To answer the refrain affirmatively would require naming a candidate — and no candidate survives the preceding verse. The question is unanswerable, and its unanswerability is repeated five times so that the hearer experiences it rather than merely concludes it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:65

> **Say, "None in the heavens or on the earth know the Unseen, save God. And they are not aware of when they will be resurrected."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Section on Knowledge Begins**

*Qul lā yaʿlamu man fī'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi'l-ghayba illa Llāh wa mā yashʿurūna ayyāna yubʿathūn* — "Say: 'None in the heavens and the earth knows the Unseen save God; and they do not perceive when they will be raised.'"

With verse 65 the sūrah moves from the five questions to its epistemological conclusion, and the transition is marked by *qul* — "say" — the third such command in the section (vv. 59, 64, 65). The five questions established what God does; verse 65 establishes what God alone *knows*, and the two claims together complete the argument against association.

**Al-Ghayb: The Word and Its Two Domains**

*Al-ghayb* is from *gh-y-b*, absence, concealment — the same root as *ghāʾib*, absent (used of the hoopoe at verse 20: *mina'l-ghāʾibīn*), and *ghaybah*, absence. The source commentary defines the Qur'anic usage precisely: *ghayb* refers to what is invisible in the sense of belonging to another order of reality, not something too distant or hidden to see physically — but it can also refer to events unknowable because they are in the remote past or have yet to occur, such as when human beings will be resurrected.

The two domains are distinct and the tradition distinguished them. The first is *ghayb* in the ontological sense: God, the angels, the jinn, the soul, the Garden, the Fire, the Preserved Tablet — realities of a different order that no instrument could reach because they are not in the instrument's domain. The second is *ghayb* in the temporal sense: what has happened and was not recorded, what will happen and has not arrived, and the hour of one's own death.

The Qur'an uses the word in both senses and states the exclusivity of God's knowledge in both:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He. And He knows what is on the land and in the sea; and no leaf falls but that He knows it, nor a grain in the darknesses of the earth, nor anything fresh or dry, but that it is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 6:59)

> *"Say: 'I do not say unto you that with me are the treasures of God, nor that I know the Unseen, nor do I say unto you that I am an angel. I follow only what is revealed unto me.'"* (Qur'an 6:50)

> *"Say: 'I have no power over benefit or harm for myself, save what God wills. And if I knew the Unseen, I would have multiplied good, and adversity would not have touched me. I am but a warner, and a bringer of good news to a people who believe.'"* (Qur'an 7:188)

> *"Knower of the Unseen and the Witnessed (ʿālim al-ghayb wa'l-shahādah); He is Exalted above what they associate."* (Qur'an 59:23)

> *"Say: 'None in the heavens and the earth knows the Unseen save God; and they do not perceive when they will be raised.'"* (Qur'an 27:65)

Verse 7:188 is the most personal statement of the exclusivity, and it is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ himself: *if I knew the Unseen, I would have multiplied good and adversity would not have touched me.* The verse forecloses, in the Prophet's own reported words, every claim that he possessed independent knowledge of the future — and the Islamic tradition has treated this verse as decisive against all forms of divination attributed to him.

**ʿĀlim Al-Ghayb Wa'l-Shahādah: The Formula of Comprehensive Knowledge**

The source commentary notes that God is described several times as "Knower of the Unseen and the Witnessed" (*ʿālim al-ghayb wa'l-shahādah*), which could also be translated "Knower of what is absent and what is present," at 6:73, 9:94, 9:105, 13:9, 23:92, 32:6, 39:46, 59:22, 62:8, and 64:18.

*Shahādah* is from *sh-h-d*, presence, witnessing, testimony — the same root discussed at verses 32 (*ḥattā tashhadūn*) and 49 (*mā shahidnā mahlika ahlihī*). The pair *ghayb / shahādah* is the Qur'an's comprehensive division of all that is: the absent and the present, the concealed and the witnessed. The formula asserts that both belong to God's knowledge, and the assertion is exclusive:

> *"That is the Knower of the Unseen and the Witnessed, the Mighty, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 32:6)

> *"He knows what is before them and what is behind them, and they encompass nothing of His knowledge save what He wills."* (Qur'an 2:255)

The second clause of 2:255 is the qualification that governs the entire doctrine: creatures may know something of the *ghayb*, but only what God wills to disclose. This is the basis on which the tradition affirms that prophets receive knowledge of the unseen *by revelation* — and it is the basis on which verse 65's exclusivity is understood: not that no creature ever knows anything hidden, but that no creature knows it *of himself*.

> *"Knower of the Unseen — He does not disclose His Unseen to anyone, save a messenger of whom He is pleased; then He dispatches before him and behind him observers."* (Qur'an 72:26–27)

Verse 72:26–27 is the decisive text on the question and it is unambiguous: the disclosure of the *ghayb* is God's prerogative, exercised toward chosen messengers, and accompanied — the verse adds — by a guarding that prevents unauthorized access.

**The Reason for the Verse's Revelation**

The source commentary records that some say this verse was revealed when the idolaters asked the Prophet ﷺ to tell them when the Day of Resurrection would come (al-Qurṭubī reports this). The question is recorded elsewhere in the Qur'an, and verse 71 of this sūrah will quote it directly:

> *"And they say: 'When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?'"* (Qur'an 27:71; cf. 10:48, 21:38, 34:29, 36:48, 67:25)

> *"Mankind asks thee concerning the Hour. Say: 'The knowledge of it is only with God; and what will make thee perceive — perhaps the Hour is near.'"* (Qur'an 33:63)

> *"They ask thee about the Hour: 'When will it arrive?' In what [position] art thou to mention it? Unto thy Lord is its term."* (Qur'an 79:42–44)

> *"They ask thee as though thou wert informed of it. Say: 'The knowledge of it is only with God, but most people know not.'"* (Qur'an 7:187)

The Hour's knowledge is withheld from every creature including every prophet, and the ḥadīth literature confirms the position with a report of unusual significance. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record the incident of Gabriel appearing as a traveler and asking the Prophet ﷺ about Islam, faith, and excellence — and then:

> *"He said: 'Inform me about the Hour.' He said: 'The one asked about it knows no more than the one asking.' He said: 'Then inform me about its signs.' He said: 'That the slave-girl gives birth to her mistress, and that you see the barefoot, naked, destitute shepherds competing in the construction of tall buildings.'"* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

*"The one asked about it knows no more than the one asking"* — *al-masʾūlu ʿanhā bi-aʿlama mina'l-sāʾil* — is the Prophetic formulation of verse 65, delivered to the angel who came to teach the religion. The report is the tradition's strongest statement that the Hour's time is not revealed knowledge, not secret knowledge, and not obtainable by spiritual advancement: Gabriel himself asked, and the answer was that neither of them knew.

**Wa Mā Yashʿurūna Ayyāna Yubʿathūn: The Second Clause**

*"And they do not perceive when they will be raised."* The verb *yashʿurūn* is the one discussed at verses 18 and 50 — from *sh-ʿ-r*, fine perception, sensation, awareness. The sūrah uses it three times: the ant reasoned that Solomon's hosts would crush them *without perceiving* (18); the nine conspirators plotted *while they did not perceive* (50); and the disbelievers *do not perceive* when they will be raised (65).

The three uses form a progression in the sūrah's treatment of perception. The ant perceives a danger that the powerful do not. The conspirators do not perceive that they are being counter-plotted. And the disbelievers do not perceive the date of their own resurrection. Each case is a failure of awareness at a different scale — and the third is the one that matters most, because the unaware person cannot prepare.

*Ayyāna* is an interrogative of time — "when, at what time" — used in the Qur'an for questions about the Hour:

> *"Does man suppose that We shall not assemble his bones? Yea, We are able to reshape his very fingertips. Nay, but man wishes to go on sinning. He asks: 'When is the Day of Resurrection?'"* (Qur'an 75:3–6)

> *"And they say: 'When is this promise, if you are truthful?'"* — and: *"They say: 'When will this judgment take place, if you are truthful?'"* — cf. 32:28, 34:29, 67:25, 79:42

*Yubʿathūn* is from *b-ʿ-th*, to raise, to send forth, to resurrect — the same root as *baʿth*, resurrection, and *mabʿūth*, one raised. The Qur'an uses the verb of both prophetic sending and of resurrection, and the double sense is deliberate: the One who sends messengers is the One who raises the dead.

**The Two Clauses as a Single Argument**

Verse 65's two clauses are not a list but an argument, and its structure is worth setting out.

The first clause establishes the exclusivity: *no one in the heavens and the earth knows the Unseen save God.* The formulation is spatially comprehensive — the heavens and the earth contain every creature, including angels and jinn — and the exception is single.

The second clause applies the exclusivity to the case the hearers are asking about: *and they do not perceive when they will be raised.* The pronoun *they* refers to those in the heavens and the earth generally, and the resurrection is the specific item of *ghayb* about which the disbelievers were questioning.

The argument therefore answers the question by denying its premise. The idolaters asked *when* the Hour will come, expecting either an answer or an admission of ignorance that would discredit the messenger. The verse answers that the question cannot be answered by any creature — not by the Prophet ﷺ, not by the priests, not by the soothsayers of Makkah who claimed knowledge of hidden things, not by the angels. The knowledge is with God alone, and the inability to state it is not a deficiency of the messenger but a feature of reality.

**The Verse's Application: The Two Kinds of Hidden Knowledge People Seek**

Verse 65 addresses a desire that is constant across cultures and centuries: the desire to know what is hidden — the future, the fate of the dead, the identity of the trustworthy, the outcome of an undertaking, the date of one's own death.

The desire produces two industries, and the verse rules on both.

The first is **divination** — astrology, haruspicy, palmistry, tarot, omens, and their modern equivalents in predictive analytics presented as certainty. The Qur'an's position is categorical and it was stated at verse 47: augury is the practice of Thamūd's people, and the Prophet ﷺ called it *shirk* (Abū Dāwūd 3910; al-Tirmidhī 1614). Verse 65 supplies the reason: the *ghayb* is not accessible, so any claim to access it is either a lie or an attribution of divine knowledge to something created.

The second is **anxiety about the future**, which is not an industry but a condition, and which the verse treats differently. The person who cannot know when he will die, whether his venture will succeed, or when the world will end is not being deprived; he is being placed in the only condition in which moral life is possible. A person who knew the date of his death would live toward that date; a person who knew the outcome of his choices would not need to make them. Verse 65's withholding of the *ghayb* is not a limitation on human beings but the condition of their freedom — and the sūrah states the corollary at verse 82: certainty that arrives without choice, after the Hour's signs, avails nothing.

The practical instruction is therefore the one the Prophet ﷺ gave in the ḥadīth of Gabriel: the Hour is not known, but its *signs* are described. The believer is not to calculate dates; he is to observe conditions and prepare. That is the same instruction verse 62 gave the *muḍṭarr* and verse 61 gave the person standing on stable ground: attend to what is disclosed, act on it, and leave the undisclosed to the One who holds it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:66

> **Nay, but does their knowledge reach to the Hereafter? Nay, but they are in doubt concerning it; indeed, they are blind to it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnosis in Three Descending Steps**

*Bal iddāraka ʿilmuhum fī'l-ākhirati bal hum fī shakkin minhā bal hum minhā ʿamūn* — "Nay, but their knowledge reaches to the Hereafter? Nay, but they are in doubt concerning it; nay, but they are blind to it."

The verse contains three clauses, each introduced by *bal* — the particle that strikes out what precedes and substitutes something else, used at verses 36, 47, 56, and 64. Three *bal*s in one verse is the Qur'an's most concentrated use of the particle, and the effect is a rapid descent through three states.

**Bal Iddāraka ʿIlmuhum: The Verb and Its Readings**

The first clause is textually the most difficult in the verse, and the source commentary records the readings. *Bal iddāraka* can be vocalized and read in several ways, depending on how the consonants are pointed.

Read as an **interrogative**, *a-ddāraka*, the clause is a question of reproach: "Does their knowledge reach to the Hereafter?" or "Or does their knowledge reach to the Hereafter?" — a rhetorical question expecting a negative answer, in the register of incredulous challenge.

Read as a **declarative**, *iddāraka*, the clause is a statement: "Their knowledge reaches to the Hereafter" — in the sense, as al-Ṭabarī explains, that they will come to possess knowledge in the Hereafter after it is too late for such knowledge to do them any good. On this reading the statement is ironic: their knowledge *will* reach the Hereafter — when they arrive in it, and see it, and know it completely, and cannot act on it.

If *iddāraka* is read as a contraction of *tadāraka* — such contractions occur in Arabic when letters whose pronunciation is close are blended together — it would mean something like "their knowledge is completed in the Hereafter" (al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī record this). *Tadāraka* is Form VI from *d-r-k*, to reach, to overtake, to catch up with, to attain — the same root as *idrak*, comprehension, and *daraka*, overtaking.

Al-Rāzī records the several readings and notes that they depend on vocalization. The translation follows the interrogative reading, which is the one most commentators prefer and which produces the sharpest sense: a question about the reach of their knowledge, answered by the two following clauses.

**The Interrogative Reading and Its Force**

On the interrogative reading, the verse asks: *does their knowledge extend to the Hereafter?* And the two following *bal*s supply the answer by descending through what their knowledge actually is.

The question's target is the disbelievers' claim to knowledge. They had said, at verse 67 and elsewhere, that the resurrection is impossible — *"What? When we and our fathers are dust, shall we indeed be brought forth?"* — and a claim of impossibility is a claim of knowledge. To assert that something will not happen is to assert knowledge of the future, which is *ghayb*, which verse 65 has just established belongs to God alone.

The verse therefore catches the disbelievers in the position verse 65 foreclosed: they claim to know about the Hereafter, and no creature knows the *ghayb*. Their claim is not merely false; it is *impossible* — it asserts an access no creature has.

**Bal Hum Fī Shakkin Minhā: The First Descent**

*"Nay, but they are in doubt concerning it."* *Shakk* is from *sh-k-k*, doubt, uncertainty — the same root as *shakk*, a cleft or fissure, and the word denotes a state of division in the mind, being pulled between two positions. Arabic's epistemological vocabulary distinguishes *shakk* (doubt, equipoise between two options) from *ẓann* (conjecture, a preponderant but uncertain opinion) from *wahm* (illusion, a belief with no ground) from *yaqīn* (certainty).

The verse places the disbelievers at *shakk* — the lowest grade, in which the mind holds both possibilities with no preponderance. This is a descent from the interrogative clause: they do not know about the Hereafter; they are uncertain about it.

But the verse's diagnosis is more damaging than a simple statement of doubt, because these people were asserting *impossibility*. A person who says "the resurrection will not happen" is not in equipoise; he has resolved the question. Verse 66 says he has not. Beneath the assertion lies unresolved doubt, and the assertion is a defense against it.

The Qur'an describes this condition elsewhere with precision:

> *"And they have no knowledge of it; they follow nothing but conjecture (ẓann), and conjecture avails nothing against the truth."* (Qur'an 53:28)

> *"Nay, but they denied what they encompassed not in knowledge, and whose interpretation had not come unto them."* (Qur'an 10:39)

> *"Did you deny My signs, though you encompassed them not in knowledge?"* (Qur'an 27:84 — this sūrah, nineteen verses later)

Verse 10:39 and verse 84 use the identical verb discussed at verse 22 — *aḥāṭa*, to encompass — and both accuse the disbelievers of denying what they had *not comprehended*. The denial precedes the knowledge, and verse 66 states what lies underneath: doubt.

**Bal Hum Minhā ʿAmūn: The Second Descent**

*"Nay, but they are blind to it."* *ʿAmūn* is the intensive plural form of *ʿamī*, from *ʿ-m-y*, blindness — and the intensive form denotes a settled, comprehensive, habitual blindness rather than a single instance. Al-Rāghib and the commentators distinguish *ʿamā* (blindness of the eye) from *ʿamah* (bewilderment, discussed at verse 4, *yaʿmahūn*); here the word is *ʿamūn*, blindness, in its intensive form.

The descent is now complete, and its three steps are: they do not know (the interrogative); they doubt (the first *bal*); they are blind (the second *bal*). Doubt implies that evidence is present and unassessed. Blindness implies that the evidence cannot be assessed at all — that the faculty itself is impaired.

The Qur'an states the same diagnosis in its most explicit form:

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth, that they might have hearts by which to understand, or ears by which to hear? Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is hearts within breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

> *"Deaf, dumb, blind — so they will not return."* (Qur'an 2:18)

> *"And if thou callest them unto guidance, they hear thee not; and thou seest them looking at thee while they see not."* (Qur'an 7:198)

> *"And We have made upon their hearts coverings, lest they understand it, and in their ears a heaviness."* (Qur'an 17:46; 6:25; 18:57)

Verse 22:46 is the decisive text: the blindness is not ocular but cardiac — *ʿamā al-qulūb*, the blindness of hearts within breasts. And its cause is stated in verse 4 of this sūrah: *We have made their deeds seem fair unto them, while they wander confused.*

**The Three Clauses as a Progressive Account of Unbelief**

Verse 66 is one of the Qur'an's most precise phenomenologies of disbelief, and its three steps describe a process rather than a state.

A person begins by not knowing the Hereafter — the *ghayb* is inaccessible to creatures, and verse 65 has established this as a fact about reality rather than as a deficiency.

Instead of accepting the inaccessibility, he resolves it by assertion: it will not happen. The assertion removes the discomfort of not knowing, and verse 67 will record it in the disbelievers' own words.

Beneath the assertion lies doubt, which the assertion exists to suppress. Verse 66 names the doubt, and by naming it removes the assertion's credibility: a person who is certain does not need to assert so loudly.

And beneath the doubt lies blindness — an impairment of the faculty that would resolve the doubt. The evidence is available (verses 60–64 listed five domains of it), and it is not being processed. Verse 4 explained why: the deeds have been adorned, and the adornment is what produces the blindness.

The three steps are therefore not three descriptions of three groups. They are three layers of one condition, and the verse peels them in a single sentence. This is why the Qur'an can say of the same people that they *know* (14: they were convinced in their souls), that they *doubt* (66), and that they are *blind* (66) — all three at once, at different depths.

**Minhā: The Preposition That Locates the Blindness**

Both the second and third clauses use *minhā* — "concerning it, from it" — referring to the Hereafter. The preposition *min* with the verb of blindness is significant: the blind are blind *from* it, that is, cut off from it, unable to receive from it. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that *min* here indicates the source from which knowledge would come: they are blind *toward* the Hereafter, so nothing arrives from it to them.

The image is of a person facing a window that he cannot see through. The Hereafter is not absent; the perception of it is absent. This is the same structure verse 22:46 describes — the eyes are open, the hearts are blind — and it explains why the Qur'an's remedy is never the provision of new evidence. Verses 60–64 provided five domains of evidence. Verse 66 says the problem is not the evidence; it is the eye.

**The Verse's Application**

The practical force of verse 66 lies in the middle clause. The first clause describes a question, the third describes a condition, and the second describes a *state most readers can recognize in themselves*: doubt.

Doubt about the Hereafter is not confined to disbelievers. Every believer experiences it — not as doctrinal rejection but as a failure of vividness. The Garden is affirmed in the creed and does not appear in the day's decisions; the Fire is recited in prayer and does not alter the hour's choices. This is not *shakk* in the technical sense, and the tradition does not treat it as unbelief. But it is the condition verse 3 identified as the third mark of the believers and named *yaqīn* — certainty — precisely because most people do not have it.

The verse supplies the diagnosis of its absence in a single word: *blindness from it*. The Hereafter is not perceived, and what is not perceived does not govern. The Qur'an's remedy for blindness is stated at verse 69 — *"Journey upon the earth and behold how the guilty fared in the end"* — and at verse 93 — *"He will show you His signs, and you will know them."* The remedy is not argument but *sight*: travel, look at the ruins, attend to the signs, and the blindness is addressed at its own level.

And the verse's warning is the one that applies most urgently to a person who asserts confidently that the Hereafter will not happen — or, in its modern form, that death is annihilation, that nothing follows, that the moral order is a projection. Verse 66 does not argue with the assertion. It asks what is underneath it, and answers: doubt, and then blindness. A reader who recognizes his own assertion in that diagnosis has been given something more useful than a refutation.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:67

> **And those who disbelieve say, "What? When we and our fathers are dust, shall we indeed be brought forth?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objection Quoted Verbatim**

*Wa qāla'lladhīna kafarū a-idhā kunnā turāban wa ābāʾunā a-innā la-mukhrajūn* — "And those who disbelieve said: 'What! When we are dust, and our fathers too, shall we indeed be brought forth?'"

Verse 66 diagnosed the disbelievers' inner state; verse 67 quotes their outer speech. The quotation is verbatim and it is preserved with its rhetorical particles intact, so that the reader hears the objection as it was spoken — with its incredulity, its interruption, and its emphasis.

**A-Idhā Kunnā Turāban: The Interrupting Particle**

The sentence opens with the interrogative *a-* followed immediately by the temporal *idhā* — "what! when we are..." The construction in Arabic produces an indignant interruption: the speaker does not wait for the proposition to be completed before objecting to it. He seizes on the first clause and breaks in.

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī analyze the construction as a mark of astonishment and denial: the objector cannot get past the premise. The same construction appears throughout the Qur'an in the mouths of the disbelievers, and the source commentary lists the parallels:

> *"What! When we are dead and dust and bones, shall we indeed be raised?"* (Qur'an 13:5)

> *"What! When we are bones and crumbled dust, shall we indeed be raised as a new creation?"* (Qur'an 17:49)

> *"What! When we die and are dust and bones, shall we indeed be raised — we and our fathers before us?"* (Qur'an 37:16–17)

> *"Does he promise you that when you die and are dust and bones, you will be brought forth? Far, far indeed is that which you are promised."* (Qur'an 23:35–36)

> *"What! When we are lost in the earth, shall we indeed be [recreated] in a new creation?"* (Qur'an 32:10)

> *"They say: 'What! Shall we indeed be returned to the [original] state? What! When we are bones crumbled?'"* (Qur'an 79:10–11)

> *"And they say: 'When we are bones and crumbled dust, shall we indeed be raised as a new creation?'"* — cf. 56:47, 17:98, 36:78

The formula's repetition across at least fifteen places is itself significant. The Qur'an preserves the objection in its original words, in multiple sūrahs, Makkan and Madinan, over more than a decade of revelation — and it never reports the objectioners as having refined their argument. The objection is always the same: we will be dust, therefore we will not return.

**Turāban: The Word for Dust**

*Turāb* is from *t-r-b*, soil, earth, dust — the same root as *tariba*, to be dusty, and *turāb*, the loose topsoil. The Qur'an uses the word of the material from which human beings were made:

> *"Verily the example of Jesus with God is as the example of Adam: He created him from dust (turāb), then He said unto him 'Be,' and he was."* (Qur'an 3:59)

> *"And among His signs is that He created you from dust (turāb), and then — behold! — you are human beings spreading abroad."* (Qur'an 30:20)

> *"O mankind, if you are in doubt about the Resurrection — verily We created you from dust (turāb), then from a drop, then from a clot, then from a lump of flesh, formed and unformed."* (Qur'an 22:5)

Verse 22:5 is the Qur'an's most complete answer to verse 67's objection, and the answer is built entirely on the objectors' own word. *You are in doubt about the Resurrection — but We created you from dust already.* The dust is not an objection to re-creation; it is the material of the original creation, and the original creation is a fact the objectors do not dispute, since they are here to dispute it.

**Wa Ābāʾunā: The Addition of the Fathers**

*"And our fathers too."* The objection extends beyond the self to the ancestors, and the extension is psychologically precise. The objectors are not merely asking about their own resurrection; they are appealing to the visible fact of their fathers' absence. Their fathers died, were buried, decayed, and did not return. The evidence of the graves is the strongest empirical argument available to them, and they use it.

The Qur'an records the same appeal in the disbelievers' mouths at 37:17: *"we and our fathers before us?"* and at 23:83:

> *"We were promised this, we and our fathers before; this is nothing but fables of those of old."* (Qur'an 23:83 — quoted at verse 68 of this sūrah)

The appeal to the fathers is the appeal to tradition and to visible evidence combined: our fathers are dust, and our fathers' fathers were dust, and no one has ever come back. The argument is inductive and it is not unreasonable on its own terms — which is why the Qur'an answers it not by dismissing it but by supplying a counter-instance the objectors accept: their own creation from the same dust.

**A-Innā La-Mukhrajūn: The Emphatic Question**

*"Shall we indeed be brought forth?"* The clause carries three markers of emphasis and interrogation: the interrogative *a-*, the particle *inna*, and the *lām* of confirmation — *a-innā la-mukhrajūn*. The construction in Arabic produces a question about a strongly asserted proposition: "is it really the case that we shall certainly be brought out?"

*Mukhrajūn* is the passive participle of Form IV from *kh-r-j*, to go out, to bring out — the same root as *nukhrijannahum* at verse 37, where Solomon threatens to expel them, and *yukhriju'l-khabʾa* at verse 25, where God brings forth what is hidden. The passive form denotes being brought out by another: they will be *extracted*, taken out of the graves.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that "brought forth" can mean something like "taken out," that is, taken out of the grave. The word choice connects verse 67 to verse 25 in a way the reader is meant to register: the God who brings out what is hidden in the heavens and the earth — the rain from the cloud, the plant from the seed, the treasure from the ground — is the God who brings out the dead from the graves. The hoopoe's argument against sun-worship was an argument about bringing forth the hidden; verse 67's objection is answered by the same verb.

**Al-Rāzī on the Root of the Doubt**

Al-Rāzī's analysis, recorded in the source commentary, identifies the underlying error: doubt in the reality of the Hereafter stems from doubt about the Power of God, which is in a sense a failure of ordinary human imagination. The Qur'an states the same diagnosis:

> *"Is not He Who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like thereof? Yea, indeed, and He is the Knowing Creator."* (Qur'an 36:81)

> *"Is not He Who created the heavens and the earth able to give life to the like of them? Yea, indeed, and He is the All-Knowing Creator."* — and: *"Verily the creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most people know not."* (Qur'an 40:57)

> *"And they have not measured God with the measure due to Him, while the whole earth is His grasp on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens are rolled up in His right hand."* (Qur'an 39:67)

> *"On the Day when the earth shall be changed into an earth other than the earth, and the heavens [into heavens other than the heavens], and they shall come forth unto God, the One, the Overpowering."* (Qur'an 14:48)

Verse 40:57 states the argument's core: the creation of the heavens and the earth is *greater* than the creation of mankind, and the objectors accept the greater while denying the smaller. Al-Rāzī's characterization of this as a failure of imagination is precise. The objectors are not reasoning badly; they are imagining badly — they have formed a mental picture of God's power on the scale of human power, and the picture cannot accommodate a resurrection.

**The Sufi Reading: Knowledge and Vision**

The source commentary records that some Sufis say that as one's knowledge of God increases, one's apprehension of certain unseen and mysterious realities increases as well (Ibn ʿAjībah) — which is a way of saying that to the degree that one is truly detached from the world, one begins to see beyond it.

The reading is coherent with verse 66's diagnosis of blindness and with the Qur'an's own statement:

> *"O you who believe! If you revere God, He will appoint for you a criterion (furqānan), and will absolve you of your evil deeds, and forgive you. And God is Possessed of tremendous Bounty."* (Qur'an 8:29)

*Furqān* — a criterion, a discriminator, a means of telling things apart — is what reverence produces. The person who guards himself against God's displeasure is given, in the tradition's account, a faculty of discrimination that others lack; and the faculty extends to realities others cannot apprehend.

This is not a claim that believers receive private revelations about the *ghayb*, which verse 65 forecloses. It is a claim about *apprehension*: the reality of the Hereafter becomes vivid to the reverent in a way it does not become to the negligent, and the vividness is a kind of knowledge — not propositional but perceptual. The disbelievers of verse 67 lack it; the believers of verse 3 have it, and verse 3 named it *yaqīn*.

**The Verse's Application: The Objection in Its Modern Form**

Verse 67's objection is not archaic, and its modern restatement is nearly word-for-word identical: we die, our bodies decompose, our atoms disperse into the biosphere, and there is no mechanism by which they could be reassembled into a person. Therefore there is no resurrection. The objection is inductive, appeals to the evidence of graves, includes the ancestors, and is expressed with the same incredulous interruption.

The Qur'an's answer, given at 22:5, 36:79, 30:27, and 50:15, does not dispute the science. It disputes the inference, and it does so on three grounds.

The first is the **priority of the original creation**: you were made from this material once, and you did not make yourself. Verse 36:78 states the point with characteristic sharpness — the objector *"forgets his own creation"* and then asks who will give life to rotten bones.

The second is the **comparative scale**: the creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the re-creation of a human being (40:57), and the objector accepts the greater. If the greater is granted, the smaller cannot be denied on grounds of difficulty.

The third is the **identity of the material**: verse 50:15 asserts that the first creation was not beyond God, and verse 17:49–51 records the objectors' question — *"when we are bones and crumbled dust, shall we indeed be raised?"* — with the answer: *"Say: 'Be stones, or iron, or any creation that seems great in your breasts' — and they will say: 'Who will restore us?' Say: 'He Who originated you the first time.'"*

The third answer is the decisive one, and it does not require the reader to imagine a mechanism. It requires him to identify the agent. The person who asks "how can dust become a person?" is asking a question whose answer he has already accepted once — in himself — and the acceptance is not theoretical. He is dust that became a person. Verse 30:20 states it as an observable fact: *"He created you from dust, and then — behold! — you are human beings spreading abroad."*

The reader who is spreading abroad has his answer in his own existence, and verse 67 is asking him whether he has noticed it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:68

> **Indeed, we were promised this, we and our fathers, before. This is naught but fables of those of old!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From Repetition**

*Laqad wuʿidnā hādhā naḥnu wa ābāʾunā min qablu in hādhā illā asāṭīru'l-awwalīn* — "Verily we were promised this — we and our fathers — before. This is nothing but fables of the first."

Verse 67 quoted the disbelievers' incredulous question; verse 68 quotes their supporting argument, and the argument deserves careful examination because it is not a trivial objection. It is a genealogical and historical claim, and it is the most sophisticated thing said against resurrection anywhere in the Qur'an.

**Laqad Wuʿidnā: The Emphatic Opening**

*"Verily we were promised."* The clause carries the *lām* of oath and *qad*, the particle of confirmation with the perfect tense — together producing one of the strongest assertions in Arabic: "we have certainly been promised." The emphasis is the emphasis of a person who believes he has accumulated experience.

*Wuʿidnā* is the passive of Form III from *w-ʿ-d*, to promise, to pledge — the same root as *waʿd*, promise, *waʿīd*, threat, *mawʿūd*, promised, and *mīʿād*, appointed time. Arabic uses the root for both good and ill promises, and the Qur'an uses it of the Garden (*waʿd*) and the Fire (*waʿīd*) alike.

The passive form is significant: *we were promised* — by someone, repeatedly, over time. The objectors are not denying that the promise was made; they are asserting that it was made often, to many people, over a long period, and never fulfilled.

**Nāḥnu Wa Ābāʾunā Min Qablu: The Cumulative Claim**

*"We and our fathers before."* The pronoun *naḥnu* — "we" — is separated and placed before the conjunction, which in Arabic produces emphasis: *we ourselves*, and our fathers too. The clause establishes two generations of waiting, and verse 67 had already invoked the fathers' dust as evidence.

The argument is therefore: this promise has been outstanding across generations. Our fathers heard it, waited for it, died, and were buried. We have heard it, and we are alive, and nothing has happened. The promise has a track record, and the track record is empty.

This is an empirical argument of a recognizable modern type: a prediction that has been made repeatedly and has never been verified has decreasing credibility with each failed interval. The Qur'an preserves the argument in the objectors' own words, with its emphatic particles intact, and does not caricature it.

**In Hādhā Illā Asāṭīru'l-Awwalīn: The Classification**

*"This is nothing but fables of the first."* The construction *in ... illā* — "nothing but, only" — is one of Arabic's strongest restrictive formulas: it excludes every alternative. *This* — the promise of resurrection — *is nothing other than* the fables of the ancients.

*Asāṭīr* is the plural of *usṭūrah* (or, on some analyses, of *isṭār* or *asṭār*), and the word denotes written lines, records, and — in the Qur'an's usage — stories, legends, tales transmitted from the past. The classical lexicons connect it to *saṭara*, to write in lines, and *miṣṭar*, a ruling instrument for drawing lines; the *asāṭīr* are the written lines, and by extension the contents of old books.

*Al-awwalīn* is the genitive plural of *al-awwal*, the first, the former — the ancients, the predecessors, the people of long ago. The phrase *asāṭīr al-awwalīn* is thus "the written tales of the ancients," and it is one of the Qur'an's most frequently quoted accusations, appearing in nine places:

> *"And when Our signs are recited unto him, he says: 'Fables of the ancients (asāṭīru'l-awwalīn).'"* (Qur'an 6:25)

> *"And when they hear, they say: 'We have heard. If we wished, we could say the like of this; this is nothing but fables of the ancients.'"* (Qur'an 8:31)

> *"Then he said: 'This is nothing but magic handed down. This is nothing but the word of a mortal.'"* — and: *"They said: 'Fables of the ancients, which he has written down; they are dictated to him morning and evening.'"* (Qur'an 25:5)

> *"What! When we are dead and are dust and bones, shall we indeed be raised — we and our fathers before us? Say: 'Yea, indeed, and you shall be among the humiliated.'"* — and the accusation: *"This is nothing but fables of the ancients."* (Qur'an 37:16–17)

> *"He said: 'We were promised this, we and our fathers, before. This is nothing but fables of the ancients.'"* (Qur'an 23:83)

> *"And they said: 'What is the matter with this Messenger? He eats food and walks in the markets. Why is not an angel sent down unto him, that he may be a warner with him?'"* — and: *"And those who disbelieve say: 'This is nothing but a lie he has invented, and another people have helped him with it.'"* (Qur'an 25:4)

> *"And when it is said unto them: 'What has your Lord sent down?' they say: 'Fables of the ancients.'"* (Qur'an 16:24)

> *"Woe on that Day to those who deny... When Our signs are recited unto him, he says: 'Fables of the ancients.'"* (Qur'an 68:15, 83:11-13)

The source commentary notes that the often-repeated idea of the dismissal of prophetic messages as *fables of those of old* is comparable to calling them legends or old wives' tales, and directs the reader to 25:4–5 for the fuller form of the accusation.

**Why the Classification Is Damning**

The accusation's force lies in what it accomplishes without argument. To classify a text as *asāṭīr al-awwalīn* is to assign it to a category that has already been discounted — the category of inherited stories, written long ago, copied forward, unverifiable, and belonging to a less informed age. Once the classification is accepted, no examination of the text's content is required.

The move is structurally identical to verse 13's accusation of *siḥrun mubīn* — manifest sorcery — and it operates by the same mechanism discussed there: reclassification as a substitute for refutation. The signs at verse 13 were *mubṣirah*, luminous and self-evident; they were called sorcery. The promise at verse 68 is supported by the argument from origination (22:5, 36:79, 40:57); it is called a legend.

Verse 25:5 gives the fullest version of the accusation and reveals its internal structure: *"fables of the ancients, which he has written down; they are dictated to him morning and evening."* The accusation is that the Qur'an is *transcribed* — copied from earlier written sources, dictated to an unlettered man on a schedule. This is a specific, falsifiable historical claim, and it is the same claim made in every century since by those who wish to dismiss the text without reading it.

**The Qur'an's Answer: The Claim of Novelty**

The Qur'an's response to the accusation is not to argue that its stories are true but to argue that they are *not* borrowed, and the argument is made in terms the accusers could verify:

> *"And thou didst not recite before it any book, nor didst thou transcribe one with thy right hand; for then the falsifiers might have doubted."* (Qur'an 29:48)

> *"That is of the tidings of the Unseen which We reveal unto thee; thou didst not know it, neither thou nor thy people, before this."* (Qur'an 11:49)

> *"And thou wast not beside the western side when We decreed unto Moses the command, nor wast thou among the witnesses."* (Qur'an 28:44)

> *"And thou wast not with them when they cast their pens, [to see] which of them should foster Mary, nor wast thou with them when they quarreled."* (Qur'an 3:44)

> *"That is a tidings of the Unseen which We reveal unto thee; for thou wast not with them when they determined their affair and plotted."* (Qur'an 12:102)

The argument's structure is precise: the reporter could not have been present, could not have read, could not have been told — yet the report contains details available only to a witness. Verse 12:102 is the Qur'an's own application of the argument to the story of Joseph, and it uses the same vocabulary of *plotting* (*yamkurūn*) that verse 50 used of Thamūd's nine.

Verse 29:48 is the decisive text on the accusation of transcription, and it answers it with a biographical fact the accusers knew: Muhammad ﷺ did not read or write before the revelation. The *asāṭīr* accusation requires a scribe; the Prophet's illiteracy removes the scribe; and the Qur'an's own statement of the fact forecloses the accusation on the objectors' own ground.

**The Answer From History: Verse 69**

The Qur'an's second answer is not textual but historical, and it comes in the very next verse: *"Journey upon the earth and behold how the guilty fared in the end."* The answer to "this is a legend about destroyed peoples" is "go and look at them."

The third answer is the argument from origination, given at 22:5, 36:78–81, 40:57, and 50:15, and discussed at verse 67 above.

And the fourth answer is the Qur'an's own quality — its linguistic inimitability, its internal consistency, and its predictive accuracy — stated at 4:82 and 2:23 and demonstrated across the text:

> *"Do they not then reflect upon the Qur'an? Had it been from other than God, they would surely have found within it much discrepancy."* (Qur'an 4:82)

> *"And if you are in doubt concerning what We sent down upon Our servant, then bring a sūrah like it."* (Qur'an 2:23)

**The Verse's Application: The Oldest Dismissal and Its Modern Forms**

Verse 68 is the oldest dismissal available to a reader of any sacred text, and it is the one that requires the least work. Its modern forms are numerous and precise: "mythology," "Bronze Age cosmology," "the product of its time," "borrowed from earlier Near Eastern literature," "a cultural artifact," "the pious fiction of a community." Each of these is a *classification*, and each accomplishes what *asāṭīr al-awwalīn* accomplished: the text is placed in a category that does not require examination.

The verse's diagnostic value for a reader is that it exposes the move in his own mind. When a person finds himself saying "that's just an old story," he should notice that he has made a claim — about the text's origin, its sources, its age, and its relation to other literature — and that the claim requires the same evidence he would demand of any other historical assertion. The objectors of verse 68 did not produce evidence for the *asāṭīr* classification. They produced a feeling of familiarity: we have heard this before, our fathers heard it, nothing happened.

Familiarity is not evidence of antiquity, and unfulfilled expectation is not evidence of falsity — particularly when the expectation concerns a date nobody was given. Verse 65 established that the Hour's time is known only to God, and verse 71 will quote the objectors demanding the date. The argument of verse 68 therefore fails on its own terms: a promise whose fulfillment date was never stated cannot be counted as repeatedly failed, and the objectors' own demand at verse 71 concedes that they do not have the date.

What they have is a disposition, and verse 66 named it: doubt beneath the assertion, and blindness beneath the doubt. The *asāṭīr* classification is the assertion that covers both.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:69

> **Say, "Journey upon the earth and behold how the guilty fared in the end!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Empirical Answer**

*Qul sīrū fī'l-arḍi fa-nẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu'l-mujrimīn* — "Say: 'Travel in the earth, then behold how was the end of the criminals!'"

Verse 68 quoted the accusation that the Qur'an's accounts of destroyed peoples are legends. Verse 69 answers by commissioning a field study. The command is in the plural — *sīrū*, *fa-nẓurū* — addressed to the objectors collectively, and it is the Qur'an's most direct invitation to empirical investigation.

**Sīrū Fī'l-Arḍ: The Command to Travel**

*Sīrū* is the imperative plural of *sāra*, to travel, to journey, to proceed — the same root as *sayr*, movement, *sīrah*, conduct and biography, and *masīr*, a journey. The phrase *sīrū fī'l-arḍ* appears in the Qur'an fourteen times, always in the same formula and always with the same purpose:

> *"Say: 'Travel in the earth, then behold how was the end of the criminals.'"* (Qur'an 27:69)

> *"Say: 'Travel in the earth and see how was the end of the deniers.'"* (Qur'an 6:11)

> *"Have they not traveled in the earth and seen how was the end of those before them? They were mightier than them in strength..."* (Qur'an 30:9; 40:21, 82; 35:44; 47:10; 12:109; 22:46; 29:20; 40:82)

> *"Say: 'Travel in the earth and see how He originated creation; then God produces the later creation. Verily God has power over all things.'"* (Qur'an 29:20)

Verse 29:20 is the most comprehensive of the series: the travel is commissioned for two purposes — to see how destroyed peoples ended, and to see *how God originated creation.* The first is history; the second is natural science. Both are given as religious duties in the imperative plural.

**Fa-Nẓurū: The Sixth Use of the Verb of Looking**

*"Then behold."* The verb *nẓarū* is the imperative plural of *n-ẓar* — the verb that has governed this sūrah's epistemology since verse 27. Solomon said *sananẓur* (we shall see) about the hoopoe's report; *fa-nẓur* (observe) about the letter's recipients; the council said *fa-nẓurī* (consider) to the queen; the queen said *fa-nāẓiratun* (I shall see) about her envoys; Solomon said *nanẓur* (we shall see) about the disguised throne; and now the reader is commanded *fa-nẓurū* (behold).

The progression is the sūrah's argument in miniature: the deliberate looking that began as a king's verification of a bird's report ends as a divine command addressed to all humanity. The instrument is the same throughout — attentive observation directed at a specific question — and its objects have escalated from a hoopoe's claim to the ruins of civilizations.

**Kayfa Kāna ʿĀqibatu'l-Mujrimīn: The Third Use of the Refrain**

*"How was the end of the criminals."* The construction is the refrain used at verse 14 (*kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu'l-mufsidīn*, "how was the end of the workers of corruption") and verse 51 (*kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu makrihim*, "how was the end of their plotting"). This is its third occurrence, and it is the first in the imperative plural addressed to the reader.

*ʿĀqibah* — the end, the outcome, what follows at the heels — was analyzed at verse 51. *Mujrimīn* is the plural of *mujrim*, the active participle of *ajrama*, Form IV from *j-r-m*, to commit a crime, to offend, to cut off — the same root as *jurm*, crime, and *ijrām*, criminality. The Qur'an uses *mujrim* as a settled designation for the persistent wrongdoer:

> *"And thus We made in every city its greatest criminals (akābira mujrimīhā), that they might plot therein."* (Qur'an 6:123)

> *"Verily the criminals (al-mujrimūn) are in the punishment of Hell, abiding."* (Qur'an 43:74)

> *"Shall We treat those who submit as the criminals? What is the matter with you? How do you judge?"* (Qur'an 68:35–36)

> *"And the day when the Hour arrives — on that Day the criminals shall despair."* — and: *"And it shall be said: 'Gather those who did wrong, and their companions, and what they used to worship.'"* (Qur'an 37:22)

Verse 6:123 is particularly relevant, since it uses the identical vocabulary verse 48 used of Thamūd's nine — the *akābir mujrimīhā*, the greatest criminals of every city, who plot therein. The sūrah has already given a case study: nine men in a city, working corruption, swearing a mutual oath, planning a night murder — and verse 51 reported their end. Verse 69 generalizes the case study into a command.

**Why the Answer to "Legend" Is "Go and Look"**

The connection between verses 68 and 69 is the most instructive feature of this passage, and its logic is exact.

The accusation at verse 68 is historical: these accounts are *asāṭīr al-awwalīn*, tales written by the ancients and transmitted. The answer at verse 69 is also historical: *travel in the earth and see how the end of the criminals was.* The Qur'an does not answer a historical claim with a theological assertion. It answers it with a direction to physical evidence.

The evidence is available and it was known to the first hearers. The Thamūd's houses were standing ruins at al-Ḥijr on the caravan route between Madinah and Syria, and verse 52 pointed at them: *"those are their houses, fallen desolate."* The Prophet ﷺ passed through al-Ḥijr on the Tabūk expedition and gave the instruction recorded in the two Ṣaḥīḥs:

> *"Do not enter upon these people who were punished unless you are weeping; and if you do not weep, then do not enter upon them, lest what befell them befall you."* (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980)

The people of Lot's cities were identified with the region of the Dead Sea, whose waters and shores the Arabian traders knew. The Egyptians' monuments were known to every caravan that passed through Sinai. The Qur'an's command to travel is a command to visit sites its hearers could visit, and the sites were the argument.

**The Two Kinds of Travel the Qur'an Commands**

Verse 29:20 makes explicit what verse 69 implies: the Qur'an commissions two kinds of observational travel, and both are religious duties in the imperative.

The first is **historical travel** — to the ruins of destroyed peoples, in order to see the *ʿāqibah*, the outcome of wrongdoing. Its purpose is moral: the observer is meant to conclude that the moral order produces consequences, and that the consequences are visible in stone.

The second is **natural travel** — to observe *how God originated creation*, in order to conclude that the Originator can originate again. Its purpose is theological: the observer is meant to move from origination to return, which is the argument verse 64 made in four words.

The two together constitute the Qur'an's empirical program, and the tradition pursued both. The geographical literature of the Muslim world — al-Masʿūdī's *Murūj al-Dhahab*, Ibn Baṭūṭah's *Riḥlah*, al-Idrīsī's *Nuzhat al-Mushtāq*, Yāqūt's *Muʿjam al-Buldān* — is in substantial part the execution of verse 69's command; and the natural-philosophical tradition from al-Kindī through Ibn al-Haytham to Ibn Sīnā is in part the execution of verse 29:20's.

Ibn Khaldūn's *Muqaddimah* opens with a methodological statement that is a direct application of verse 69: the historian must examine the reports of the past against the observable laws of civilization, and must travel to verify what he cannot verify from books. The discipline of *isnād* criticism, the geography of the *riḥlah fī ṭalab al-ʿilm* (the journey in search of knowledge), and the empirical methods of the Muslim natural philosophers all stand under this command.

**The Verse's Application: The Ruins Within Reach**

The command *sīrū fī'l-arḍ* is addressed in the plural, and it is not restricted to a particular journey. Every reader lives within reach of evidence of the kind the verse describes, and the evidence is usually closer than the reader supposes.

Every city contains neighborhoods that were prosperous and are not. Every family contains a story of a fortune lost, a reputation destroyed, a marriage ended, a health squandered — and in each case the sequence is the one verses 45–53 described: a demand, a refusal, a conspiracy or a habit, an outcome, a ruin. Every profession has its *akābir mujrimīhā* and its houses fallen desolate. The command is not to travel to Arabia; it is to look at what one passes daily and ask the refrain's question: *how was the end?*

The verse supplies the method and the question, and it supplies a caution as well. The looking is *fa-nẓurū* — deliberate, attentive, directed looking, not casual noticing. Al-Qurṭubī records the interpretation that "behold" here means observe with one's heart and one's insight, not merely with the eyes. A person can travel to al-Ḥijr and see geology; a person can walk through his own city and see real estate. The verse requires the second kind of sight, and verse 66 explained why: the problem is not the evidence but the eye.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:70

> **And grieve not for them, and be not distressed by what they plot.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Consolation of the Messenger**

*Wa lā taḥzan ʿalayhim wa lā takun fī ḍayqin mimmā yamkurūn* — "And grieve not for them, and be not in distress because of what they plot."

Verse 69 commanded the Prophet ﷺ to tell his opponents to travel and look. Verse 70 turns from them to him, and addresses his heart. The shift is abrupt and it is characteristic of the Qur'an's method with its bearer: the messenger is given the message to deliver, and then given the care the delivery requires.

**Wa Lā Taḥzan ʿAlayhim: The Prohibition of Grief**

*"And grieve not for them."* The verb *taḥzan* is from *ḥ-z-n*, sorrow, grief, sadness — the same root as *ḥuzn*, grief, *ḥazan*, to sadden, and *maḥzūn*, grieved. The Qur'an uses the root of the Prophet's ﷺ sorrow repeatedly, and it prohibits it repeatedly:

> *"And grieve not over them, and be not in distress because of what they plot."* (Qur'an 16:127 — the closest parallel, in almost identical words)

> *"It may be that thou art killing thyself with grief, because they believe not in this discourse, in sorrow."* (Qur'an 18:6)

> *"And grieve not for those who disbelieve, and be not in distress because of what they plot."* — cf. 27:70

> *"Say: 'O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, despair not of the mercy of God.'"* — and: *"So be patient; verily the promise of God is true, and let not those who have no certainty cause thee to waver."* (Qur'an 30:60)

> *"And indeed We know that thy breast is straitened by what they say."* (Qur'an 15:97)

> *"And be patient, and thy patience is only by God; and grieve not for them, and be not in distress because of what they plot."* (Qur'an 16:127)

Verse 18:6 is the most intimate of these and it is worth quoting in full context:

> *"It may be that thou art killing thyself (bākhiʿun nafsaka) with grief, because they do not believe in this discourse. If We willed, We would send down upon them a sign from the sky, and their necks would remain bowed before it."* (Qur'an 26:3–4)

*Bākhiʿ* is from *b-kh-ʿ*, to destroy oneself, to consume oneself with effort or grief — an intensive form denoting self-destruction. The Qur'an describes its Prophet ﷺ as on the verge of destroying himself with sorrow over his people's unbelief, and verse 26:3–4 adds the reason no sign is sent to compel them: a compelled bowing of the neck is not faith.

Verse 15:97–99 supplies the remedy prescribed for the straitened breast:

> *"And indeed We know that thy breast is straitened by what they say. So glorify thy Lord with praise, and be among those who prostrate, and worship thy Lord until the certainty comes unto thee."* (Qur'an 15:97–99)

The three prescribed acts — *tasbīḥ* with praise, prostration, and worship until certainty arrives — are the Qur'an's own treatment for the grief verse 70 prohibits.

**The Reason for the Prohibition**

The commentators explain why the Prophet ﷺ is told not to grieve, and their explanations converge on a theological point of considerable importance.

Al-Rāzī's analysis, recorded at verses 80–81 of this sūrah, is the most developed: when one hopes to get something from others, it weakens one's ability to manifest opposition to them; by absolving the Prophet of any responsibility or power over whether his people believe or disbelieve, God strengthens the Prophet's ability to declare and teach the truth. The grief is therefore not merely painful; it is *disabling*. A messenger who grieves over his audience's rejection has a stake in their acceptance, and a stake in acceptance produces accommodation.

The Qur'an states the principle directly:

> *"So obey not the disbelievers, and strive against them with it [the Qur'an], a great striving."* (Qur'an 25:52)

> *"And if thou obeyedst most of those on the earth, they would lead thee astray from the way of God."* (Qur'an 6:116)

> *"Then do not obey the deniers. They wish that thou wouldst compromise, so that they might compromise."* (Qur'an 68:8–9)

Verse 68:9 is the sharpest statement of the danger grief creates: *they wish you would soften, so that they may soften.* The accommodation is mutual and it is what the audience seeks. The prohibition of grief is therefore a protection of the message, not only a comfort to the messenger.

**Wa Lā Takun Fī Ḍayqin: The Prohibition of Constriction**

*"And be not in distress."* The noun *ḍayq* is from *ḍ-y-q*, narrowness, constriction, tightness — the same root as *ḍayyiq*, narrow, and *ḍāʾiq*, constrained. The source commentary notes that *ḍayyiq* has the sense of contraction or constriction.

The word is used in the Qur'an of both physical and psychological states:

> *"And indeed We know that thy breast is straitened (yaḍīqu ṣadruka) by what they say."* (Qur'an 15:97)

> *"And [recall] the three who were left behind, until the earth, vast as it was, became strait for them, and their own souls became strait for them, and they were certain that there is no refuge from God except unto Him. Then He turned unto them, that they might turn."* (Qur'an 9:118)

> *"And whoever turns away from My Reminder — verily his shall be a straitened life (maʿīshatan ḍankā), and We shall raise him on the Day of Resurrection blind."* (Qur'an 20:124)

Verse 9:118 is the Qur'an's most powerful description of constriction — the earth vast and yet narrow, the self narrow within itself — and its resolution is the recognition that there is no refuge from God except unto Him. Verse 20:124 gives the general law: turning away from the Reminder produces a constricted life, however spacious the circumstances.

Verse 70's prohibition is thus not against sadness alone but against *constriction* — the state in which the chest tightens, the options seem to narrow, and the mind cannot expand to take in what it needs to take in. The constriction is what produces accommodation, because a constricted person seeks relief, and relief is offered by the audience in exchange for softening.

**Mimmā Yamkurūn: "Because of What They Plot"**

*"Because of what they plot."* The verb *yamkurūn* is the same one used at verse 50 of Thamūd's nine — *wa makarū makran wa makarnā makran* — and the connection is deliberate and immediate.

The nine men of Thamūd plotted a night assassination and were destroyed with their people. The nine are long dead and their houses are ruins, and verse 52 pointed at them. Now the same verb is used of the plotting against the Prophet ﷺ in Makkah, and the same answer is assumed: verse 50's *wa makarnā makran wa hum lā yashʿurūn*.

The Qur'an states the assurance explicitly elsewhere:

> *"And when those who disbelieved plotted against thee to confine thee, or to kill thee, or to drive thee out — they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 8:30)

> *"Or do they plot a scheme? Then those who disbelieve shall be the ensnared."* (Qur'an 52:42)

> *"And they plotted, and We plotted, while they did not perceive."* — and: *"So behold how was the end of their plotting: We destroyed them and their people all together."* (Qur'an 27:50–51)

Verse 8:30 was revealed about the actual conspiracy to kill the Prophet ﷺ on the night of the Hijrah, and it uses the identical vocabulary of verse 50. The prohibition of distress in verse 70 is therefore grounded in the precedent established at verse 50: plotting is answered by plotting, and the answer is not perceived by the plotters.

**The Consolation Verses as a Genre**

The source commentary notes that the Qur'an offers consolation to the Prophet ﷺ elsewhere — 5:68, 15:97, 16:127, 18:6, 26:3, 35:8 — instructing him not to be saddened by the failure of his people to embrace his mission, but to entrust their souls to God.

The passages form a coherent set, and their recurrence across Makkan and Madinan revelation indicates that the grief was recurrent too. Verse 35:8 is among the most direct:

> *"Wouldst thou then destroy thyself with grief over their footsteps, because they believe not in this discourse?"* — and: *"Verily God sends astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills; so let not thyself perish in grief over them. Verily God is Knower of what they do."* (Qur'an 35:8)

Verse 5:68 states the messenger's actual duty:

> *"Say: 'O People of the Book! You stand on nothing until you establish the Torah and the Gospel and what has been sent down unto you from your Lord.' And what has been sent down unto thee from thy Lord will surely increase many of them in insolence and disbelief; so grieve not over the disbelieving people."* (Qur'an 5:68)

The principle across all these verses is a definition of the messenger's responsibility: he delivers, and the delivery is complete whether or not it is accepted. The outcome belongs to God. Verse 92 of this sūrah will state the same principle in the Prophet's ﷺ own commissioned words: *"Verily, I am but a warner."*

**The Verse's Application: Grief Over Other People's Choices**

Verse 70 addresses a condition that is not restricted to prophets. Every person who cares about others' spiritual or moral state experiences it: a parent whose child has left the faith, a spouse whose partner will not change, a teacher whose student will not listen, a friend whose friend is destroying himself, a preacher whose congregation does not respond, a reformer whose society refuses reform.

The grief is natural and, within limits, it is a mark of love. The Qur'an does not prohibit love for the guidance of others; it prohibits the two states that love produces when it exceeds its bounds — grief that consumes (*ḥazan*) and constriction that disables (*ḍayq*). And it prohibits them for a reason that applies to every case: the grief does not help the person grieved for, and it damages the person grieving.

The verse supplies three instruments for the condition.

The first is **the precedent of verse 50**: they plot, and We plot, and they do not perceive. The person who grieves over another's choices is usually trying, in imagination, to do what only God can do — to change a heart from outside. Verse 50 states that there is a counter-arrangement operating that nobody perceives, and that the arranger is not the grieving person.

The second is **the analysis at verse 4**: those who do not believe in the Hereafter have their deeds adorned for them, and they wander confused. The adornment is divine and the wandering is its consequence; the grieving person is not the cause of either and cannot be the cure.

The third is **the practice prescribed at 15:98–99**: glorify with praise, prostrate, and worship until certainty comes. The constriction is treated not by argument with the constriction but by acts — the praise, the prostration, the continuing worship — and by the arrival of *yaqīn*, which is the certainty verse 3 named as the mark of the believers.

Verse 70 is therefore not a command to stop caring. It is a command to relocate the care: from the grief that consumes and the distress that narrows, to the delivery that is complete in itself, the plotting that is answered elsewhere, and the worship that continues until certainty arrives.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:71

> **And they say, "When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for a Date**

*Wa yaqūlūna matā hādhā'l-waʿdu in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — "And they say: 'When will this promise [come to pass], if you are truthful?'"

Verse 68 quoted the accusation that the promise is a legend; verse 71 quotes the demand that accompanies it. The two verses together give the complete structure of the objection: the promise is old, it has never been fulfilled, and its proponents cannot say when it will be.

**Matā Hādhā'l-Waʿd: The Question's Form**

*"When is this promise?"* The interrogative *matā* — when, at what time — is used for questions about time, and the ellipsis of the verb (*yaʾtī*, "will come") is characteristic of spoken Arabic in a challenge: the question is too impatient to complete itself. The effect in the original is a curt, dismissive demand — "when is it, then?"

*Al-waʿd* is the verbal noun of *w-ʿ-d*, promise — the same root as *wuʿidnā* at verse 68. The word is definite: *this* promise, the specific one you have been making. The Qur'an uses *al-waʿd* for the promised Day in numerous places, and the demand is quoted in almost identical words across the revelation:

> *"And they say: 'When will this promise [come to pass], if you are truthful?'"* (Qur'an 27:71; 21:38; 34:29; 36:48; 67:25)

> *"And they say: 'When is this promise? Tell us, if you know.'"* — cf. 10:48: *"And they ask thee: 'Is it true?' Say: 'Yea, by my Lord, verily it is true, and you are not the ones who can escape.'"*

> *"They would have thee hasten the punishment; and were it not for a term appointed, the punishment would surely have come upon them — and it shall surely come upon them suddenly, while they do not perceive."* (Qur'an 29:53)

> *"Mankind asks thee concerning the Hour. Say: 'The knowledge of it is only with God; and what will make thee perceive — perhaps the Hour is near.'"* (Qur'an 33:63)

> *"They ask thee about the Hour: 'When will it arrive?' In what [position] art thou to mention it? Unto thy Lord is its term."* (Qur'an 79:42–44)

The source commentary identifies the promise as the end of the world, the coming of the Hereafter, and God's judgment of human beings, and directs the reader to 36:48 for the parallel treatment.

**In Kuntum Ṣādiqīn: The Challenge to Truthfulness**

*"If you are truthful."* The plural *kuntum* addresses the Prophet ﷺ and the believers together — or, on some readings, the Prophet ﷺ and the previous messengers whose promises are invoked. The condition *in kuntum ṣādiqīn* uses the same verb Solomon employed at verse 27 (*a-ṣadaqta*) and the same one demanded at verse 64 (*in kuntum ṣādiqīn*, "if you are truthful").

The structure is a challenge to substantiate a claim, and the specific claim challenged is *truthfulness*. The objectors are not asking for evidence of the resurrection; they are asking for a date, on the reasoning that a truthful person who knows a thing will be able to say when it occurs.

The reasoning contains a hidden premise, and the Qur'an's answer attacks the premise rather than the question. The premise is that a truthful reporter of a future event must know its timing. The answer is that the timing was never part of the report: the messengers were commanded to announce the event, not its date, and the date was withheld by the One who commissioned them.

**Verse 65 as the Answer Already Given**

The Qur'an has already answered this question in this very sūrah, six verses earlier, and the answer is verse 65:

> *"Say: 'None in the heavens or on the earth knows the Unseen, save God. And they are not aware of when they will be resurrected.'"* (Qur'an 27:65)

The connection between verse 65 and verse 71 is exact and structural. Verse 65 states the exclusivity of God's knowledge of the *ghayb* and specifies the second item as *when they will be raised*. Verse 71 quotes the demand for precisely that item. The answer is already in the text, and verse 72 will deliver it.

The ḥadīth of Gabriel states the same answer in the Prophet's ﷺ own words:

> *"He said: 'Inform me about the Hour.' He said: 'The one asked about it knows no more than the one asking.'"* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8)

And the tradition's treatment of every subsequent claim to know the Hour's date follows from this. From the earliest generations to the present, individuals and movements have announced dates for the end of the world, and the Muslim scholarly establishment has rejected every one of them on the authority of verse 65 and the ḥadīth of Gabriel. Ibn Kathīr states the position in his commentary on the signs of the Hour: what is known is the sequence of the signs, not the interval between them, and the interval is known only to God.

**The Question's Function as an Objection**

The demand for a date is not a neutral inquiry, and the Qur'an treats it as an objection rather than a question. Verse 72's answer — *"it may be that some of what you seek to hasten is close behind you"* — reveals the demand's actual character: they were *hastening* (*tastaʿjilūn*), which is the same verb Ṣāliḥ used of Thamūd at verse 46: *"why do you hasten the evil before the good?"*

The parallel is exact and the sūrah intends it. Thamūd demanded that Ṣāliḥ bring the punishment if he was truthful (7:77, and v. 46 above). Makkah demands the date of the promise if the Prophet ﷺ is truthful (v. 71). Both are the same move: converting a moral warning into a testable prediction, so that the failure of the prediction to arrive on demand refutes the warning.

Verse 13:6 states the general pattern:

> *"And they would have thee hasten the evil before the good, though examples have passed before them."* (Qur'an 13:6)

*"Though examples have passed before them"* — *wa qad khalat mina'l-muthulātu min qablihim* — the punishments of the earlier peoples had already occurred, and the objectors knew of them. Their demand to hasten was therefore not ignorance of the possibility but a confidence that it would not happen to them.

Verse 29:53–55 gives the fullest treatment:

> *"They would have thee hasten the punishment; and were it not for a term appointed, the punishment would surely have come upon them — and it shall surely come upon them suddenly, while they do not perceive. They would have thee hasten the punishment; and verily Hell shall encompass the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 29:53–54)

The phrase *wa hum lā yashʿurūn* — "while they do not perceive" — appears again, for the fourth time in this sūrah (vv. 18, 50, 65, and here by parallel). The objectors demanding the date are the people who will not perceive the arrival.

**Why the Date Is Withheld**

The withholding of the Hour's date is not an evasion, and the Qur'an gives the reason in terms that make the withholding a mercy and a discipline.

The first reason is **moral**: a known date would change the character of faith. If the Hour were announced for a specific year, every person would repent in the final month, and the repentance would be compelled rather than free. Verse 6:158 states the principle:

> *"Do they await anything but that the angels should come unto them, or that thy Lord should come, or that some of the signs of thy Lord should come? On the Day when some of the signs of thy Lord come, no soul's faith will profit it, if it did not believe before, or earned good in its faith."* (Qur'an 6:158)

The ḥadīth recorded by Muslim states the same:

> *"There are three things which, when they appear, no soul's faith will profit it if it did not believe before, or earned good in its faith: the rising of the sun from its place of setting, the Dajjāl, and the beast of the earth."* (Muslim 158)

Verse 82 of this sūrah will discuss the beast, and the ḥadīth binds it to the closing of the door of repentance. The withholding of the date is what keeps the door open.
