The second reason is **epistemic**: the date belongs to the *ghayb*, and verse 65 has established that no creature knows the *ghayb* except by disclosure. Its non-disclosure is a fact about the structure of knowledge, not a deficiency in the message.

The third reason is **pastoral**: uncertainty about the time produces vigilance about the state. A person who does not know when the examination will be studies constantly; a person who knows the date studies the night before. The Prophet ﷺ said, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a traveler on a road."* (al-Bukhārī 6416)

And ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar added the practical formulation: *"If you are in the evening, do not wait for the morning; and if you are in the morning, do not wait for the evening. Take from your health for your illness, and from your life for your death."* (al-Bukhārī 6416)

The instruction presumes exactly what verse 71's objectors demand be removed: the uncertainty about when. The uncertainty is the instrument, not the obstacle.

**The Verse's Application: The Demand for Dates in Every Age**

Verse 71's demand is not confined to seventh-century Makkah. It recurs in two forms in every generation, and both are answered by the same verses.

The first form is the **skeptical demand**: give us a date, and if you cannot, the promise is a legend. This is the form verse 68 and verse 71 take together, and the answer is verse 65 — the date was never claimed, by any prophet, at any time, and the person who claims it now is not speaking for the revelation.

The second form is the **believer's demand**: when will it be, so that I can prepare? This form is not objectionable, and it is answered differently. The preparation does not require the date; the date would remove the preparation's value. The ḥadīth supplies the answer to the sincere version of the question, in the report recorded by al-Bukhārī:

> *"A man asked the Prophet ﷺ: 'When is the Hour?' He said: 'What have you prepared for it?' The man said: 'Nothing, except that I love God and His Messenger.' He said: 'Thou shalt be with whom thou lovest.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3688; Muslim 2639)

*"What have you prepared for it?"* — *mā aʿdadta lahā* — is the Prophetic response to every sincere form of the demand for a date. The question is redirected from the time of the event to the state of the questioner, and the redirection is the answer. Anas ibn Mālik, the narrator, added: *"The Muslims did not rejoice over anything after Islam as they rejoiced over this statement."*

Verse 71 quotes the objection; verse 72 answers it; and the answer will not be a date.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:72

> **Say, "It may be that some of what you seek to hasten is close behind you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to the Demand for a Date**

*Qul ʿasā an yakūna radifa lakum baʿḍu'lladhī tastaʿjilūn* — "Say: 'Perhaps there has followed close upon you some of that which you seek to hasten.'"

Verse 71 demanded a date. Verse 72 answers without giving one, and the answer is one of the Qur'an's most compressed warnings. It does not say when; it says that part of what is being demanded may already be at your back.

**ʿAsā An Yakūna: The Particle of Near Expectation**

*ʿAsā* is a particle expressing expectation, hope, or near likelihood — "perhaps, it may be, perchance, likely." Al-Ṭabarī explains that when *ʿasā* is used of God or in divine speech, it carries the sense of what is expected and often of what is certain, since God's expectation is not speculation. The Qur'an uses the particle of its own promises:

> *"It may be (ʿasā) that thy Lord will raise thee to a praised station."* (Qur'an 17:79)

> *"It may be that God will bring about affection between you and those of them with whom you were at enmity."* (Qur'an 60:7)

> *"And it may be that you dislike a thing and it is good for you; and it may be that you love a thing and it is evil for you. And God knows, and you know not."* (Qur'an 2:216)

The commentators note that *ʿasā* in the speech of God indicates nearness rather than doubt. The particle therefore does not weaken the warning; it sharpens it. "Perhaps" here means "it is close enough that you should act as though it has begun."

**Radifa Lakum: The Verb of Following at the Nape**

*Radifa* is from *r-d-f*, to follow, to ride behind, to be at the back of — the same root as *ridf*, the one who rides behind on a mount, and *ardafa*, to give a ride behind, and *tarādafa*, to follow in succession. The classical lexicons gloss *radifahu* as "he followed close behind him, as one riding behind another on the same animal."

The image is precise and physically vivid: something is mounted behind you, on the same animal, at your back, close enough to touch, and you cannot see it. Al-Ṭabarī explains that *is close behind* means "has come near," and al-Qurṭubī adds the sense of following in someone's footsteps.

The Qur'an uses the same root elsewhere:

> *"On the Day when the Quaker quakes, and the Follower (al-rādifah) follows it."* (Qur'an 79:6–7)

> *"The two recording [angels] record, sitting on the right and on the left."* — and: *"And verily over you are watchers (ḥāfiẓīn), noble, recording"* (Qur'an 82:10–11)

> *"Say: 'He is the Able to send upon you a punishment from above you or from beneath your feet, or to confuse you in factions and make you taste the violence of one another.'"* (Qur'an 6:65)

Verse 79:7's *al-rādifah* — the follower, the second quaking — is the Qur'an's use of the same root for the eschatological event that follows the first, and the pairing of the two quakings with *radifa* at verse 72 produces a resonance: the thing that follows close behind is the second shaking.

**Baʿḍu'lladhī Tastaʿjilūn: "Some of What You Seek to Hasten"**

The object of the warning is not the whole of what they demanded but *some* of it — *baʿḍ*, a part. The precision is significant and the commentators developed it.

Al-Rāzī records the interpretation that what is closing in upon them is their defeat at the Battle of Badr, which occurred within a few years of the revelation of this sūrah; and the source commentary notes that it is common among commentators for such warnings of impending punishment to be interpreted as references to Badr. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī both mention this reading, and it was widely held because Badr was the first fulfillment — the moment at which the leadership of Makkan paganism was killed or captured, exactly as the Qur'an had promised.

The Qur'an's own statement of the Badr promise is at 8:7 and 3:12, and the connection to verse 5 of this sūrah was noted there: some commentators situate *"a terrible punishment"* in this world, seeing it as a reference to Badr. Verse 72's *baʿḍ* — some of what you seek to hasten — is thus read by many as the worldly portion of the punishment, arriving before the eschatological portion.

The reading has a general form that is more important than the specific identification. What the objectors sought to hasten was the whole promised package: the resurrection, the judgment, the punishment. Part of that package had a worldly component — defeat, loss, humiliation, death — and the worldly component arrived first, at Badr and afterward, on the schedule the objectors did not choose.

The warning's structure is therefore: you demand the whole thing on your terms and by a date; part of it is already at your back, on God's terms, and you do not perceive it.

**Tastaʿjilūn: The Verb of Hastening, Returned**

The verb is Form X from *ʿ-j-l*, to seek haste, to demand speed — the identical verb Ṣāliḥ used at verse 46: *"why do you hasten the evil before the good?" (lima tastaʿjilūna bi'l-sayyiʾati qabla'l-ḥasanati)*. The verbal echo across twenty-six verses connects Makkah to Thamūd with a single word, and the connection is the sūrah's method: the objectors of every age perform the same act, and the act has the same name.

The Qur'an uses the verb of the demand repeatedly:

> *"They would have thee hasten (yastaʿjilūnaka) the punishment; and were it not for a term appointed, the punishment would surely have come upon them."* (Qur'an 29:53)

> *"And they say: 'O Thou upon whom the Reminder has been sent down, verily thou art possessed.'"* — and: *"They would have thee hasten the punishment, and verily Hell shall encompass the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 29:54)

> *"And they ask thee to hasten the punishment; and God will not fail His promise. And verily a day with thy Lord is as a thousand years of what you reckon."* (Qur'an 22:47)

Verse 22:47 supplies the temporal frame that makes verse 72's warning comprehensible: the divine schedule operates on a scale at which a thousand human years are one day. The objectors demanding a date are demanding that the schedule be expressed in their units, and the warning is that the schedule is already operating in units they cannot see — which is why the thing is *at their back* rather than in front of them.

**The Warning's Twofold Force**

Verse 72 is usually read as a threat, and it is one. But it has a second force that the commentators note, and the second is an invitation.

As a **threat**, the verse says: what you mock is close, and part of it has already begun. The objectors demanding the date were, within a few years, killed at Badr or living under a Muslim polity in Madinah; within twenty years the whole of Arabia had changed; and the sūrah's warning was verified in the lifetime of its hearers.

As an **invitation**, the verse says: there is still time to do something about it. A warning delivered before the arrival is a warning that can be heeded. The Qur'an states the principle at 8:33:

> *"And God would not punish them while thou wast among them, nor would God punish them while they sought forgiveness."* (Qur'an 8:33)

And Ṣāliḥ's proposal at verse 46 was exactly this: seek forgiveness, that you may be shown mercy. Verse 72's *ʿasā* — perhaps, it may be — holds the door open by declining to specify. The warning is severe precisely because it is not a verdict; a verdict would close the matter.

**The Verse's Application: What Is at Your Back**

The practical force of verse 72 does not depend on identifying Badr, and it applies to every reader in every age. The verse states a general law: *some of what you dismiss as distant is already following close behind you.*

The law operates in several registers at once.

**Individually**: the death every person speaks of as future is nearer than his next plan. The Qur'an states the proximity explicitly:

> *"And We are nearer to him than [his] jugular vein."* (Qur'an 50:16)

> *"Say: 'Verily the death from which you flee — it shall surely meet you; then you shall be returned to the Knower of the Unseen and the Witnessed, and He will inform you of what you used to do.'"* (Qur'an 62:8)

Verse 62:8 is verse 72's principle applied to the individual: the thing you flee will meet you, and the meeting is certain while its time is unknown. The correct response to that combination is not the calculation of the time but the preparation of the state — which is the Prophetic answer at al-Bukhārī 3688: *"What have you prepared for it?"*

**Collectively**: the consequences a society dismisses as theoretical are usually already in motion. Verse 34's political theory — kings entering towns, corrupting them, abasing their honored people — describes a process the participants do not perceive while it operates. Verse 4's adornment describes a society's own deeds made attractive to it while the wandering continues. The things a civilization calls exaggeration are frequently the things already at its back, and verse 69's command to travel and look at the ruins is the method for verifying this.

**Spiritually**: the hardening of the heart that a person postpones dealing with is not postponed. Verse 66 described the descent from doubt to blindness, and the descent operates while the person believes he has time. The Qur'an's warning about the process is explicit:

> *"Has the time not come for those who believe, that their hearts should be humble at the remembrance of God and at what has come down of the truth — and that they should not be as those who were given the Book before, and a term was prolonged for them, so their hearts hardened, and many of them are dissolute?"* (Qur'an 57:16)

*"Has the time not come"* — *a-lam yaʾni* — is the same question verse 71 asked in mockery, turned by the Qur'an into a genuine inquiry addressed to believers. The answer, in the tradition's reading of 57:16, is that the time had already come and passed, and the hardening was in progress. That is what *radifa lakum* means in the individual register: the thing you are treating as future is riding behind you now.

**The Verse's Refusal to Give a Date**

There is a final feature of verse 72 worth noting, because it defines the Muslim posture toward eschatology. The verse could have answered the demand. It does not. It answers with a particle of near expectation, a verb of following, and a quantifier of part — *perhaps, close behind, some of* — and it gives no date, no sequence, and no calendar.

The refusal is doctrinal and it has consequences. A tradition that does not know the date cannot schedule its righteousness, cannot postpone its repentance to a calculated time, and cannot declare itself to be living in the last generation. The Muslim world has produced eschatological expectation continuously for fourteen centuries, and the mainstream scholarly position has consistently been the one verse 72 establishes: the signs are described, the intervals are not, and the arrival is nearer than the observer supposes.

That is not a counsel of vagueness. It is a counsel of *immediacy without calculation* — the state of a person who lives as though the thing at his back could touch him at any moment, and who therefore does not need to know when.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:73

> **And truly thy Lord is Possessed of Bounty toward mankind, but most of them do not give thanks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Names the Human Default**

*Wa inna rabbaka la-dhū faḍlin ʿalā'l-nāsi wa lākinna aktharahum lā yashkurūn* — "And verily thy Lord is Possessed of Bounty toward mankind, but most of them do not give thanks."

Verse 72 warned that the demanded punishment was close behind. Verse 73 turns from the warning to the situation that makes the warning necessary: mankind is being treated with bounty and is not responding with gratitude. The verse is the sūrah's diagnosis of the human condition in eight words, and it is the hinge between the eschatological section (vv. 65–72) and the section on God's knowledge (vv. 74–75).

**Dhū Faḍlin: The Possessor of Bounty**

*Dhū faḍl* — "Possessed of Bounty" — is one of the Qur'an's characteristic descriptions of God, and the source commentary notes its appearance at 2:243, 2:251, and 40:61:

> *"And know that God is Knowing, Wise."* — and: *"And had it not been for the Bounty of God upon you (faḍlu Llāhi ʿalaykum) and His mercy, you would have followed Satan, save a few."* (Qur'an 4:83)

> *"And God singles out for His mercy whom He wills; and God is Possessed of tremendous Bounty (dhū'l-faḍli'l-ʿaẓīm)."* (Qur'an 2:105)

> *"That is the Bounty of God (faḍlu Llāh); He gives it unto whom He wills. And God is Possessed of tremendous Bounty."* (Qur'an 62:4; 57:21)

> *"And verily thy Lord is Possessed of Bounty toward mankind (la-dhū faḍlin ʿalā'l-nās), but most of them do not give thanks."* (Qur'an 27:73)

> *"And thy Lord is Possessed of Bounty toward mankind, but most of them do not give thanks."* (Qur'an 40:61)

*Faḍl* is from *f-ḍ-l*, excess, preference, bounty — the same root as *faḍḍala* at verse 15 ("He favored us above many of His servants") and *al-faḍl al-mubīn* at verse 16 ("clear bounty") and *min faḍli rabbī* at verse 40 ("of the Bounty of my Lord"). The word is thus the sūrah's central term for what God gives, and verse 73 is its doctrinal statement: God *is* Possessed of Bounty toward mankind — not that He sometimes shows bounty, but that bounty is His standing disposition toward the species.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī explain *faḍl* as giving beyond what is deserved and beyond what is required — the excess over the obligatory. The concept is essential to the Qur'an's theology of reward: the reward for a good deed is not a payment but a *faḍl*, and the multiplication of ten to seven hundred times (2:261) is bounty rather than arithmetic.

> *"Whoever brings a good deed shall have ten times the like thereof; and whoever brings an evil deed shall be recompensed only with the like thereof, and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 6:160)

Verse 6:160 states the asymmetry precisely: good is multiplied, evil is matched. The multiplication is *faḍl*; the matching is justice. Verse 40 of this sūrah established the same principle from the other side — *"whoever gives thanks, he gives thanks only for his own soul"* — and verse 89 will state it again: *"whoever brings a good deed shall have that which is better than it."*

**ʿAlā'l-Nās: Toward Mankind, Including the Ungrateful**

The preposition *ʿalā* with *al-nās* — "upon mankind" — is comprehensive, and its comprehensiveness is the verse's most difficult and most important claim. The bounty is not restricted to believers. It is toward *mankind*, and mankind includes the objectors of verses 67–68 and 71, the plotters of verse 70, the disbelievers of verse 66, and the people whose deeds were adorned at verse 4.

The Qur'an states the universality explicitly:

> *"And He gave you of all that you asked Him; and if you would count the bounty of God, you could not number it. Verily man is unjust, ungrateful."* (Qur'an 14:34)

> *"And if you would count God's favor, you could not number it. Verily God is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 16:18)

> *"And We have honored the children of Adam, and We carried them on the land and the sea, and We provided them with good things, and We preferred them above much of what We created."* (Qur'an 17:70)

Verse 17:70 is the Qur'an's statement of human dignity as a *given* — conferred on the children of Adam as such, not earned by faith or virtue. The honor, the transport, the provision, and the preference are all *faḍl* toward mankind, and verse 73 names the disposition that produces them.

The tradition developed this into a distinction of considerable importance: God's *raḥmah ʿāmmah*, general mercy, which extends to all creatures in this world — believer and disbeliever, human and animal — and His *raḥmah khāṣṣah*, particular mercy, which is for the believers in the Hereafter. Verse 73 describes the first, and its comprehensiveness is why the verse can say *mankind* without qualification.

**Wa Lākinna Aktharahum Lā Yashkurūn: The Diagnosis**

*"But most of them do not give thanks."* The particle *lākinna* — "but, however" — introduces a contrast with emphasis, and the contrast is the verse's content: bounty is given to all, and gratitude is returned by few.

*Aktharahum* — "most of them" — is the same qualification used at verse 61 (*bal aktharuhum lā yaʿlamūn*, "most of them know not"). The Qur'an's use of "most" is deliberate and consistent: it does not say all, and the exception is real. There is a grateful minority, and verse 3 described it.

The source commentary lists the parallels stating that most human beings are not sufficiently grateful: 2:243, 7:10, 7:17, 10:60, 12:38, 23:78, 32:9, 40:61, and 67:23. The recurrence of the statement across the revelation is itself significant — the Qur'an returns to it because it is the central fact about the human response to God.

> *"And few of My servants are grateful (wa qalīlun min ʿibādiya'l-shakūr)."* (Qur'an 34:13)

> *"Say: 'It is He Who originated you and made for you hearing and sight and hearts. Little do you give thanks.'"* (Qur'an 67:23)

> *"And God sets forth a parable: a town that was secure and at ease, its provision coming to it abundantly from every side; then it was ungrateful for God's favors, so God made it taste the garment of hunger and fear for what they used to do."* (Qur'an 16:112)

Verse 34:13 is the Qur'an's own quantification — *few* — and it appears in the context of Solomon and David, the two men of verses 15–19, who were commanded: *"Work, O house of David, in gratitude. And few of My servants are grateful."* The command and the observation are joined in one verse, and al-Naml's account of David and Solomon is the illustration of the few.

**Lā Yashkurūn: The Verb of Gratitude**

*Yashkurūn* is from *sh-k-r*, gratitude, thankfulness — the same root as *shukr*, thanks, *shākūr*, grateful (an intensive form), and *tashakkara*, to give thanks. The Qur'an uses the verb of gratitude to God and makes it the pivot of the human condition:

> *"If you give thanks, I will surely increase you; and if you are ungrateful, verily My punishment is severe."* (Qur'an 14:7)

> *"And God sets forth a parable of two men..."* — and: *"And he said: 'This is of the Bounty of my Lord, to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful.'"* (Qur'an 27:40)

> *"And be grateful for the favor of God, if it is Him you worship."* (Qur'an 16:114)

> *"And We will reward the grateful (al-shākirīn)."* (Qur'an 3:144, 145)

> *"Verily the soul is a commander of evil, save that upon which my Lord has mercy"* — and the Qur'an's use of *shakūr* as a Divine Name at 35:30, 42:23: *"that He may increase them of His bounty. Verily He is Forgiving, Grateful (Shakūr)."*

The Name *al-Shakūr* applied to God is one of the Qur'an's most remarkable usages: God is *grateful*, in the sense that He acknowledges and multiplies the small deed of the servant. The relation is reciprocal in form though not in measure — the servant gives thanks and God gives increase, and both are described with the same root.

**Why Gratitude Is Rare: The Analysis**

The Qur'an's repeated statement that most people are ungrateful is a claim about human psychology, and the tradition analyzed the mechanism at length.

The first cause is **adaptation**. A blessing received repeatedly ceases to register. The Qur'an names the mechanism at verse 4 — the adornment of one's own deeds and circumstances — and Ibn ʿAṭāʾillāh formulates it in the *Ḥikam*: God veiled the blessing in the habit so that the heedless would take it for granted, and He removes the blessing so that the servant would recognize it. The person who has never lacked water does not experience water as a gift.

The second cause is **misattribution**. The blessing is attributed to one's own effort, intelligence, or position. The Qur'an gives the paradigm case in Qārūn:

> *"He said: 'I was given it only by knowledge I possess.'"* (Qur'an 28:78)

Verse 28:78 is from al-Qaṣaṣ, the sūrah immediately following al-Naml, and it names the exact error verse 73 diagnoses. Qārūn attributed his wealth to his own knowledge, and the verse continues: *"Did he not know that God had destroyed before him generations mightier than him in strength and greater in accumulation?"* The misattribution produces ingratitude, and ingratitude produces the loss.

Verse 39:49 gives the full analysis:

> *"And when harm touches man, he calls upon Us; then when We confer upon him a favor from Us, he says: 'I was given it only by knowledge.' Nay, but it is a trial — yet most of them know not."* (Qur'an 39:49)

*"It is a trial"* — *hiya fitnah* — and the word connects verse 39:49 to verse 40 of this sūrah: *to try me whether I will give thanks or be ungrateful.* The blessing is a trial; the trial's question is gratitude; and most people fail it by attributing the blessing to themselves.

The third cause is **inattention**. Gratitude requires noticing, and noticing requires the deliberate attention verse 66 said was missing — *qalīlan mā tadhakkarūn*, how little you remember. Verse 19's prayer is the remedy: *awziʿnī an ashkura niʿmatak* — hold me in the formation of gratitude — because the unaided self will not remain attentive.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 73 states two facts, and the reader can verify both.

The first is that he is being treated with bounty. The verification is not difficult: he is reading, which requires eyes, a mind, a language, a text, and time; he is alive, which requires air, water, food, and a body that has not failed; he is in a place, which requires shelter; and none of these were produced by him. Verse 60 established the point about a single tree, and verse 14:34 makes it comprehensive — *"if you would count the bounty of God, you could not number it."*

The second is that he probably does not give thanks for most of it. The verification is equally easy: how often today has he acknowledged, in words or in the heart, the water, the light, the health, the safety, the mind? The Qur'an's diagnosis — *most of them do not give thanks* — is not a claim about other people. It is a claim about the human default, and the reader who checks his own day will find it confirmed.

The verse's practical instruction is the one verse 19 already gave, and the sūrah's structure places them as a pair. Verse 19 is the prayer: *hold me in gratitude, for the blessing given to me and my parents, and let me work righteousness that pleases You, and admit me by Your mercy among Your righteous servants.* Verse 73 is the diagnosis: *your Lord is Possessed of Bounty toward mankind, and most of mankind does not thank Him.* The prayer is the remedy for the diagnosis, and it is available to anyone who says it.

The Prophet ﷺ took it from Solomon and gave it to Muʿādh, as recorded at verse 19: *"O God, help me to remember You, to thank You, and to worship You well"* (Abū Dāwūd 1522; al-Nasāʾī 1303). The duʿāʾ is the practical form of verse 73's correction, and it is recited by Muslims at the end of every prayer — which is the frequency the diagnosis requires.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:74

> **And surely thy Lord knows that which their breasts conceal and that which they disclose.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse on Interior Knowledge**

*Wa inna rabbaka la-yaʿlamu mā tukinnu ṣudūruhum wa mā yuʿlinūn* — "And verily thy Lord knows what their breasts conceal and what they disclose."

The verse is short, emphatic, and comprehensive. It carries *inna* and the *lām* of confirmation — "and verily thy Lord *certainly* knows" — and it divides the whole of human conduct into two categories: what is hidden inside and what is shown outside. Both are known.

**Mā Tukinnu Ṣudūruhum: What the Breasts Conceal**

*Tukinnu* is from *k-n-n*, to hide, to conceal, to shelter — the same root as *kinn*, a shelter, and *akanna*, to hide something within. The verb denotes concealment *inside* something, as opposed to concealment behind something; a thing *mukann* is hidden in a container, in the interior.

*Ṣudūr* is the plural of *ṣadr*, the chest, the breast — and in the Qur'an's usage the breast is the location of the interior life: thought, intention, feeling, belief, and secret. The source commentary notes that the concealing of thoughts and intentions within breasts is an important idea in the Qur'an, discussed frequently in the context of God's knowledge of what lies within:

> *"And God knows what is in your breasts (mā fī ṣudūrikum)"* — and the formula appears at 3:119, 3:154, 5:7, 8:43, 11:5, 29:10, 31:23, 33:51, 39:7, 40:19, 42:24, 57:6, 64:4, 67:13, 100:10

> *"And whether you conceal your speech or disclose it — verily He knows what is in the breasts."* (Qur'an 67:13)

> *"And thy Lord knows what their breasts conceal and what they disclose."* (Qur'an 28:69 — the closest parallel, cited by the source commentary)

> *"O you who believe! Fear God, and let every soul look to what it has sent forward for the morrow."* — and: *"And God is Aware of what you do"* — cf. 59:18

Verse 28:69 is nearly identical to our verse, and it appears in al-Qaṣaṣ — the sūrah immediately following al-Naml. The two sūrahs share this statement at their conclusions, which is one of the many seams joining the three Ṭā-Sīn chapters.

**The Qur'an's Vocabulary of the Interior**

The Qur'an uses several terms for the interior life, and the tradition distinguished them with care:

*Ṣadr* — the chest, the breast: the container of the interior life. Used in *sharḥ al-ṣadr*, the expansion of the breast (94:1), and *ḍīq al-ṣadr*, the constriction of the breast (11:12, 15:97).

*Qalb* — the heart: the organ of understanding and of turning. *"Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts go blind"* (22:46). *"Verily in that is a reminder for him who has a heart"* (50:37).

*Fuʾād* — the heart as the seat of perception and emotion, often plural: *"And do not follow what thou hast no knowledge of; verily hearing and sight and the heart (al-fuʾād) — all of that shall be questioned"* (17:36).

*Nafs* — the self, the soul: *"And We are nearer to him than his jugular vein"* (50:16); *"Verily the soul is a commander of evil"* (12:53).

*Sarāʾir* — the innermost things, the secrets: *"On the Day when the secrets (al-sarāʾir) are tested"* (86:9).

Verse 74 uses *ṣudūr*, the containers, and the choice is deliberate: the verse is about *concealment*, and the breast is where concealment happens. Verse 86:9 supplies the eschatological corollary: on the Day, the *sarāʾir* — the innermost things — are *tested*, examined, brought out.

**Wa Mā Yuʿlinūn: What They Disclose**

*"And what they disclose."* *Yuʿlinūn* is Form IV from *ʿ-l-n*, to be open, public, manifest — the same root as *ʿalāniyah*, publicly, and *iʿlān*, declaration. The verb appeared at verse 25 in the hoopoe's argument: *wa yaʿlamu mā tukhfūna wa mā tuʿlinūn* — "and He knows what you hide and what you disclose."

The pairing at verse 25 used *tukhfūna* (you hide) and *tuʿlinūn* (you disclose), both addressed in the second person. Verse 74 uses *tukinnu ṣudūruhum* (their breasts conceal) and *yuʿlinūn* (they disclose), both in the third person, and the first is attributed to the *breasts* rather than to the people. The shift is significant: the concealment is not a deliberate act of the person but a property of the interior itself. The breast conceals; the person may not even know the full contents of his own.

The Qur'an states this elsewhere with startling precision:

> *"And He knows what is in the breasts."* (Qur'an 3:119, 5:7, 31:23, 42:24, 57:6, 64:4, 67:13)

> *"Does he not know that God sees?"* (Qur'an 96:14)

> *"And We know what his soul whispers to him, and We are nearer to him than his jugular vein."* (Qur'an 50:16)

Verse 50:16 is the Qur'an's most intimate statement of divine knowledge: *We know what his soul whispers to him* — *mā tuwaswisu bihī nafsuh* — the interior monologue, the passing thought, the half-formed intention. And the nearness is closer than the jugular, which is the nearest thing to a person's own awareness.

**The Verse's Function in the Argument**

Verse 74 follows verse 73's diagnosis of ingratitude, and its function is to complete the diagnosis with the reason no excuse will be available.

Most people do not give thanks (v. 73). The ingratitude is largely invisible: it consists of unspoken attributions, unacknowledged blessings, private self-sufficiency, and interior claims of *I was given it only by knowledge I possess* (28:78). Nobody can see another person's ingratitude, and the ingrateful person usually cannot see his own.

Verse 74 states that the seeing is being done. The breast's concealment is concealed from others and from the self, but not from God; and the disclosure — the public speech, the outward act, the visible conduct — is equally known. There is no region of the person outside the knowledge, and therefore no region in which the ingratitude can be located safely.

The verse thus prepares for verse 75, which extends the knowledge from human interiors to the whole of creation, and for verses 83–90, where the accounting occurs. The sequence is: bounty given (73), gratitude withheld (73), interiors known (74), everything recorded (75), the gathering (83), the question (84), the silence (85), the deed brought (89–90).

**The Two Effects of the Verse on a Reader**

The verse produces two opposite effects depending on the reader's state, and both are intended.

For the **person concealing something**, the verse is a warning. Every human being has interior content he would not disclose — an intention, a resentment, a plan, a desire, a dishonesty, a private judgment about another person. Verse 74 states that the content is known, and it is known by the One who will judge. The Prophet ﷺ's instruction about the interior is explicit, recorded by Muslim:

> *"God does not look at your forms or your wealth; He looks at your hearts and your deeds."* (Muslim 2564)

And:

> *"Verily deeds are by intentions, and every man shall have only what he intended."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907)

The second report establishes that the interior is not merely known but *decisive*: the deed's value is determined by the intention concealed in the breast. Verse 74 is therefore not only a statement of surveillance but a statement of what will be weighed.

For the **person wronged secretly**, the verse is a consolation. Injustice is frequently invisible: the reputation undermined in private, the credit taken by another, the harm done where no witness was present, the slander circulated in a room the victim never entered. Verse 74 states that the concealment does not protect the perpetrator, and the ḥadīth quoted by al-Qurṭubī at verse 62 supplies the corollary: *"Be mindful of the supplication of the oppressed, for there is no veil between it and God."*

The two effects are the same fact experienced from two positions, and the verse does not indicate which position the reader is in. That is left to him.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:75

> **And there is nothing hidden in Heaven or on the earth, but that it is in a clear Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Record of Everything**

*Wa mā min ghāʾibatin fī'l-samāʾi wa'l-arḍi illā fī kitābin mubīn* — "And there is not a hidden thing in the heaven and the earth but that it is in a clear Book."

Verse 74 established that God knows the interiors of human beings. Verse 75 extends the knowledge to everything hidden anywhere, and it locates that knowledge in a written record. The verse is the Qur'an's most comprehensive statement of the doctrine of the Preserved Tablet, and it is one of the most consequential verses in the sūrah for its eschatology.

**Mā Min Ghāʾibatin: The Double Negation of Comprehensiveness**

The construction *mā min ... illā* — "there is not any ... except" — is one of Arabic's most emphatic forms of universal negation followed by exception, and it produces exhaustive comprehensiveness: not a single instance is excluded.

The same construction appears in the Qur'an's parallel statements:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He. And He knows what is in the land and the sea; and not a leaf falls but that He knows it, nor a grain in the darknesses of the earth, nor anything fresh or dry, but that it is in a clear Book (illā fī kitābin mubīn)."* (Qur'an 6:59)

> *"And there is no creature on earth but that its provision is upon God; and He knows its dwelling place and its place of deposit. All is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 11:6)

> *"And no female conceives, nor delivers, but with His knowledge; and no one whose life is prolonged is prolonged, nor is his life diminished, save in a Book."* (Qur'an 35:11)

> *"And We have recorded everything in a clear Register (imāmin mubīn)."* (Qur'an 36:12)

Verse 6:59 is the closest parallel, and it ends with the identical phrase — *illā fī kitābin mubīn* — using the same adjective that verse 1 used of the Qur'an itself (*kitābin mubīn*, "a clear Book"). The connection is deliberate and the commentators draw it: the Book in which all hidden things are recorded is of the same order as the Book revealed to the Prophet ﷺ, and both are *mubīn*.

**Ghāʾibatin: The Feminine Form of the Hidden**

*Ghāʾibah* is the feminine form of the active participle of *gh-y-b*, absence, concealment — the same root as *ghayb*, the Unseen, discussed at verse 65, and *ghāʾib*, absent, used of the hoopoe at verse 20.

The source commentary notes that *hidden* renders *ghāʾibah*, related to *ghayb*. The feminine form here functions substantively — "a hidden thing" — and the grammarians note that the *tāʾ marbūṭah* in such forms often produces a single-instance sense: not "hiddenness" in general but "a hidden thing," each individual instance of it.

The effect of the negation plus the feminine singular is exhaustive at the level of the individual item: *not a single hidden thing* — not a category, not a class, but each instance — *in the heaven and the earth, except that it is in a clear Book.*

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī explain the scope: the phrase covers everything absent from human knowledge — the events of the past not recorded, the events of the future not arrived, the interior states of creatures, the matters of the Unseen discussed at verse 65, and the decrees governing all of them.

**Fī'l-Samāʾi Wa'l-Arḍ: The Spatial Comprehensiveness**

*"In the heaven and the earth."* The two locations together constitute the Qur'an's standard formula for the whole of created space, and the formula is exhaustive: there is no third location. The verse at 6:59 uses *fī'l-barri wa'l-baḥr* — "in the land and the sea" — for the same purpose at a smaller scale, and verse 65 of this sūrah used *man fī'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — "whoever is in the heavens and the earth" — for the comprehensiveness of the knowledge's exclusivity.

The combination of the two comprehensivenesses — every hidden thing, in every location — produces the verse's claim: nothing concealed anywhere is unrecorded.

**Kitābin Mubīn: The Clear Book**

*"A clear Book."* *Mubīn* is the adjective analyzed at verse 1 — from *b-y-n*, separation, distinction, clarity; active in form, meaning both *clear in itself* and *making things clear*. Its application here to the heavenly record is the same as its application at verse 1 to the Qur'an.

The commentators identify the Book with several referents, and the source commentary records the principal ones:

**The Preserved Tablet** (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*), of which the Qur'an speaks at 85:22:

> *"Nay, it is a glorious Qur'an, in a Preserved Tablet (lawḥin maḥfūẓ)."* (Qur'an 85:21–22)

**The Mother of the Book** (*umm al-kitāb*), mentioned at 3:7 and 43:4, and identified by al-Ṭabarī as the referent here:

> *"It is He Who sent down unto thee the Book, wherein are verses clear (muḥkamāt) — they are the Mother of the Book (umm al-kitāb) — and others ambiguous."* (Qur'an 3:7)

> *"And verily it is in the Mother of the Book with Us, lofty, wise."* (Qur'an 43:4)

**The Clear Register** (*imām mubīn*) of 36:12:

> *"Verily We revive the dead, and We record what they sent forward and their traces; and everything We have enumerated in a clear Register."* (Qur'an 36:12)

**The Book of Deeds** which each person will receive on the Day:

> *"And the Book shall be placed, and thou shalt see the criminals fearful of what is in it, and they shall say: 'Woe unto us! What a Book is this! It omits not a small thing nor a great, but has numbered it.' And they shall find what they did present [before them]. And thy Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

The commentators generally hold that these are not four different books but four aspects or four names of one reality, and al-Ṭabarī's identification of the *kitāb mubīn* with the *umm al-kitāb* is the majority position.

**The Doctrine of the Record**

Verse 75 states a doctrine that the Qur'an develops across many passages, and the doctrine has three components.

**Everything is written before it occurs.** The decree (*qadar*) precedes the event:

> *"No calamity befalls in the earth or in yourselves but that it is in a Book before We bring it into being. Verily that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 57:22)

> *"Verily We created everything by decree (bi-qadar)."* (Qur'an 54:49)

> *"And there is not a thing but that its treasures are with Us, and We send it not down save by a known measure."* (Qur'an 15:21)

The ḥadīth literature states the same:

> *"God ordained the measures (aqdār) of creation fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water."* (Muslim 2653, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr)

> *"The first of what God created was the Pen, and He said to it: 'Write.' It said: 'My Lord, what shall I write?' He said: 'Write the measures of all things until the Hour is established.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 4700; al-Tirmidhī 2155, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**Everything done is recorded as it occurs.** The deeds are written by appointed agents:

> *"And verily over you are watchers (ḥāfiẓīn), noble, recording (kātibīn), who know whatever you do."* (Qur'an 82:10–12)

> *"When the two recording [angels] record, sitting on the right and on the left. He utters no word but that there is with him an observer ready."* (Qur'an 50:17–18)

> *"And the Book shall be placed"* (Qur'an 18:49)

**Everything recorded will be produced.** The record is the instrument of the accounting:

> *"And every man's augury have We fastened to his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a Book which he shall find wide open: 'Read thy Book! Thy own soul suffices as a reckoner against thee this Day.'"* (Qur'an 17:13–14)

Verse 17:13 uses the word *ṭāʾir* — discussed at verse 47 as the augury the Thamūd's people took from birds — and it states that a person's *ṭāʾir* is fastened to his own neck and produced as a book. The superstition of verse 47 is answered by the record of verse 75: the omen is not in a bird; the record is in a Book, and the Book is yours.

**The Verse's Function at the Center of the Sūrah's Conclusion**

Verse 75 stands at a structural juncture, and its placement governs everything that follows. Verses 59–64 asked five questions about God's acts; verses 65–72 addressed knowledge of the Unseen and the demand for a date; verses 73–75 diagnose ingratitude and establish comprehensive knowledge and record; verses 76–81 return to the Qur'an and the Prophet's ﷺ duty; and verses 82–93 present the eschatological conclusion.

The record of verse 75 is what makes the conclusion of verses 83–90 possible. A gathering (v. 83) requires a basis for judgment; a question (v. 84) requires known answers; a silence (v. 85) requires a record that speaks when the accused cannot; and the weighing of a good deed against an evil deed (vv. 89–90) requires that both have been registered.

Verse 75 supplies all of it in one sentence, and the sentence's adjective — *mubīn*, clear — is the guarantee. The record is not ambiguous, not partial, not subject to interpretation. It is *clear* in the same sense that verse 1's Book is clear: nothing in it is obscure, and nothing in it can be argued with. Verse 18:49 gives the criminals' own reading of it: *"What a Book is this! It omits not a small thing nor a great, but has numbered it."*

**The Verse's Application: Living Inside a Record**

Verse 75 asks the reader to conceive of his life as a document being written. The conception is not metaphorical in the Qur'an's framework; it is literal, and its practical effects are considerable.

The first effect is on **the private act**. A person alone, unseen, doing what no one will ever know — the verse states that the act is registered. The Prophet ﷺ defined the standard of the private act in the report recorded by Aḥmad and al-Ṭabarānī:

> *"The best of deeds is that which is done when no one sees, and the best of remembrance is the hidden."* — and: *"Whoever is pleased by his good deed and distressed by his evil deed, he is a believer."* (Aḥmad; al-Tirmidhī 2165, graded ḥasan)

The second effect is on **the concealed injury**. A person harmed in secret, defamed in a room he did not enter, cheated in a transaction nobody examined, will find the harm in the Book. Verse 74 said the interiors are known; verse 75 says everything hidden anywhere is recorded. The two verses together are the Qur'an's answer to invisible injustice, and the answer is not that the injustice will be avenged in this world but that it will not be lost.

The third effect is on **the estimation of small things**. Verse 18:49 says the Book *"omits not a small thing nor a great."* The reader who believes this will treat the small act differently: the word spoken carelessly, the coin withheld, the glance, the silence at a moment when speech was required. Verse 99:7–8 states the arithmetic:

> *"So whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)

And the fourth effect is on **the estimation of one's own memory**. Human memory is selective, self-protective, and unreliable; verse 75's Book is none of these. Verse 17:14 supplies the sentence that will be addressed to every reader: *"Read thy Book! Thy own soul suffices as a reckoner against thee this Day."* The record will be read by the one who lived it, and the reading will not require an external prosecutor — the soul will supply the reckoning.

That is the verse's final claim, and it is the claim that makes al-Naml's conclusion possible: everything is written, everything will be produced, and the one who reads it will recognize it.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:76

> **Verily, this Qur'ān recounts unto the Children of Israel most of that wherein they differ.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Qur'ān as the Resolution of an Inherited Dispute**

*Inna hādha'l-Qurʾāna yaquṣṣu ʿalā Banī Isrāʾīla akthara'lladhī hum fīhi yakhtalifūn* — "Verily this Qur'an narrates to the Children of Israel most of that wherein they differ."

The verse turns from the doctrine of the Record to a statement about the Qur'an itself, and the statement is addressed — remarkably — to a community not present in Makkah in any significant number. The sūrah is Makkah's; its hearers are the Quraysh; and verse 76 speaks of what the Qur'an narrates to the Children of Israel.

The commentators explain the address in several ways, and all of them are instructive. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī hold that the Qur'an narrates the matters in dispute among the Israelites *to* them in the sense that its account reaches them, whether by direct encounter or by transmission; the Qur'an's narrative is addressed to the community whose disagreements it resolves. Al-Rāzī adds the broader reading recorded in the source commentary: the verse is also interpreted to mean that the Qur'an provides universal answers regarding the nature of God.

**Yaquṣṣu: The Verb of Narrating**

*Yaquṣṣu* is from *q-ṣ-ṣ*, to narrate, to recount, to follow a trace — the same root as *qiṣaṣ*, the story or narrative, *qaṣaṣ*, the narration, *iqtassa*, to follow a track, and *qāṣṣ*, the storyteller. The root's literal sense is to cut and to follow a cut line, hence to trace and to recount in order.

The Qur'an uses the verb of its own narration repeatedly:

> *"We narrate unto thee (naquṣṣu ʿalayka) the best of stories in what We have revealed unto thee of this Qur'an, though before it thou wast among the heedless."* (Qur'an 12:3)

> *"This is of the tidings of the Unseen which We reveal unto thee. Thou wast not with them when they cast their pens, [to see] which of them should sponsor Maryam, nor wast thou with them when they disputed."* (Qur'an 3:44)

> *"And We did not send before thee any but men whom We inspired; so ask the people of the Reminder if you know not."* — and: *"And We narrate unto thee (wa naquṣṣu ʿalayka) of the messengers what will make thy heart firm; and there has come unto thee in this the truth and an admonition and a reminder for the believers."* (Qur'an 11:120)

Verse 11:120 supplies the reason for the narration, and it is the reason operative in verse 76 as well: the narrating makes the Prophet's ﷺ heart firm. Verse 76's narration to the Children of Israel is a continuation of the same act, and verse 79 will connect it to the Prophet's ﷺ firmness on the manifest truth.

Verse 12:3 is the more direct parallel, and the connection is close: the Qur'an calls its own narrative *the best of stories* (aḥsana'l-qaṣaṣ) and states that the Prophet ﷺ was among the heedless before it — meaning he did not know these stories. Verse 28:44–46 makes the same point at greater length:

> *"And thou wast not on the western side when We decreed unto Moses the matter, nor wast thou among the witnesses... And thou wast not dwelling among the people of Madyan, reciting unto them Our signs; but We were senders."* (Qur'an 28:44–45)

> *"And thou wast not beside the Tur when We called; but [thou art sent] as a mercy from thy Lord, that thou mayest warn a people unto whom no warner came before thee, that haply they may remember."* (Qur'an 28:46)

The argument across all these verses is the one verse 29:48 made explicit: the Prophet ﷺ could not have recited a Book before this, nor written it with his right hand; therefore the narrative is not his, and its accuracy about matters he could not have known is its own evidence. Verse 76 places that argument in the context of Israelite disagreement.

**Akthara'lladhī Hum Fīhi Yakhtalifūn: "Most of That Wherein They Differ"**

The quantifier is *akthar* — most, not all. The verse does not claim to narrate every disputed matter, and the qualification is consistent with the Qur'an's own statement of its selectivity:

> *"And of every thing We have narrated unto thee some, that We may make thy heart firm thereby"* — cf. 11:120

> *"And verily We have repeated for mankind in this Qur'an every parable, but most of mankind refuse aught but disbelief."* (Qur'an 17:89)

> *"And We have detailed in this Qur'an every kind of thing (kulla shayʾ), that haply they may remember."* (Qur'an 39:27)

The combination of *kulla shayʾ* at 39:27 with *akthar* at 27:76 is not a contradiction but a distinction of kind: the Qur'an addresses every *kind* of thing required for guidance while narrating only *most* of the particular disputed matters. The tradition drew the inference that the Qur'an's purpose is guidance rather than exhaustive information — a principle with consequences for how the Qur'an's narratives are read.

**The Matters in Dispute**

The source commentary identifies the content of the disagreement precisely: *that wherein they differ* is what the Israelites themselves disagree about, to the point of disparaging and cursing one another about it, regarding such matters as what they and the Christians "distorted" (*taḥrīf*; see 2:75) in the Torah and the Gospel, and the obligations of the Divine Law.

The Qur'an's vocabulary for this dispute is extensive, and the categories can be organized.

**Distortion** (*taḥrīf*) of the received texts:

> *"Among those who are Jews are some who distort (yuḥarrifūna) the words from their places, and say: 'We hear and disobey,' and 'Hear — may thou not hear!' and 'Rāʿinā,' twisting their tongues and attacking the religion."* (Qur'an 4:46)

> *"Dost thou hope that they will believe in thee, when a party among them used to hear the Word of God, then distort it (yuḥarrifūnah) after they had understood it, and they knew?"* (Qur'an 2:75)

> *"And among those who are Jews are listeners to falsehood, listeners to another people..."* (Qur'an 5:41)

**The nature of God and the Messiah**:

> *"They have disbelieved who say: 'God is the Messiah, son of Maryam'"* (Qur'an 5:17, 72)

> *"And the Jews say: 'Uzayr is the son of God'; and the Christians say: 'The Messiah is the son of God.'"* (Qur'an 9:30)

**The obligations of the Law** — the question the source commentary directs to 3:50–51:

> *"[I have come] confirming the Torah that was before me, and to make lawful unto you some of that which was forbidden unto you."* (Qur'an 3:50)

> *"Verily God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him. This is a straight path."* (Qur'an 3:51)

**The Sabbath and other observances**:

> *"And verily you know those among you who transgressed in the matter of the Sabbath"* (Qur'an 2:65)

**The prophets and their killing**:

> *"That is because they disbelieved in the signs of God and killed the prophets without right"* (Qur'an 2:61, 3:21, 3:112, 3:181)

Verse 3:99 and 3:23 state the dispute's character directly:

> *"O People of the Book! Why do you disbelieve in the signs of God, while you witness?"* (Qur'an 3:70)

> *"Hast thou not considered those who were given a portion of the Book? They are invited to the Book of God that it may judge between them, then a party among them turns away, and they are averse."* (Qur'an 3:23)

Verse 3:23 is the situation verse 76 addresses: the People of the Book are invited to their own Scripture as the arbiter and a party turns away. The Qur'an's account claims to supply the arbitration.

**The Method of the Qur'anic Correction**

Verse 76's claim is not merely that the Qur'an narrates but that its narration *resolves*. The method has four components, each visible in al-Naml's own narratives.

**It narrates the events with detail the disputants lack.** Verses 7–14 gave Moses at the fire, the voice, the staff, the hand, and the mission; verses 15–19 gave David and Solomon; verses 46–53 gave Ṣāliḥ and the nine men; verses 54–58 gave Lot. The Israelite traditions about these figures were disputed — the status of Aaron, the fate of Solomon, the character of David, the content of the mission — and the Qur'an's accounts state a position.

**It corrects the record where the traditions had corrupted it.** The most consequential example is Solomon. Verses 15–44 present him as prophet, king, grateful, and dependent on God; the Israelite traditions of the period presented him as fallen, idolatrous in his old age, and led astray by his wives. The Qur'an addresses the charge directly elsewhere:

> *"And they followed what the devils recited in the reign of Solomon. And Solomon disbelieved not; but the devils disbelieved, teaching mankind sorcery..."* (Qur'an 2:102)

Verse 2:102 is the Qur'an's explicit exoneration of Solomon against a specific Israelite tradition, and al-Naml's extended portrait is the same act performed through narrative. Verse 30:30 supplies the principle: *"So set thy face toward the religion, upright"* — and verse 38:30 makes the point about Solomon in a name: *"And We bestowed upon David, Solomon — an excellent servant. Verily he was ever turning [to God] (awwāb)."*

**It affirms what the earlier revelations had genuinely taught.** The Qur'an's relation to the Torah and Gospel is not only corrective:

> *"And We sent down unto thee the Book in truth, confirming what was before it of the Book, and as a witness over it (muḥayminan ʿalayhi)."* (Qur'an 5:48)

*Muhaymin* — guardian, witness, overseer — is the word that defines the relation, and verse 76 is its application to the disputes: the Qur'an confirms what is genuine, corrects what is corrupted, and supplies what was omitted.

**It leaves the final judgment to God.** Verse 78 states this immediately after, and the pairing is the verse's essential qualification: the Qur'an narrates the resolution, but the adjudication between the disputants belongs to God on the Day. The narrator is not the judge.

**Banī Isrāʾīl: The Addressees of al-Naml**

The Children of Israel are named at verse 76 as the community to whom the Qur'an narrates, and their presence in the sūrah is structural rather than incidental. Al-Naml's narratives are dominated by Moses (vv. 7–14), David and Solomon (vv. 15–44), and the Israelite tradition about both. Verse 2 gave the Qur'an as guidance and glad tidings to the believers; verse 76 gives it as narration to the Children of Israel; and verse 77 returns to the believers.

The three statements describe one Book from three angles: a guide for those who accept, a record of correction for those who already have a scripture, and a mercy for those who believe. Al-Naml's structure — Moses, then Solomon, then the Qur'an's own claim — enacts the same sequence.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 76 has a general application that does not require the reader to be an Israelite or to be engaged in first-century Arabian scriptural disputes. The principle it states is that **the revelation resolves disagreement by narrating what actually happened**.

The principle matters because religious disagreement is frequently about *narrative* rather than about argument. Two communities dispute a prophet's character; the dispute cannot be settled by logic because it is not a logical question — it is a question about what occurred. Verse 76's answer is that the Qur'an supplies the narrative, and that its supply of the narrative to a man who did not know it is itself a ground for accepting it.

For the modern reader the verse has a second application. He lives among communities whose disagreements about the Bible, about Jesus, about Paul, about the history of doctrine, and about the character of the biblical figures are numerous and unresolved. Verse 76 states that a narration exists which addresses most of those disagreements, that it came to a man who could not have produced it, and that it is available. Whether he accepts it is verse 77's question, and verse 78's answer — that the adjudication is God's — is available to him whether or not he does.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:77

> **And verily it is a guidance and a mercy for the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Names of the Qur'ān's Effect**

*Wa innahū la-hudan wa raḥmatun li'l-muʾminīn* — "And verily it is a guidance and a mercy for the believers."

Verse 76 described the Qur'an's function toward the Children of Israel: narration resolving dispute. Verse 77 describes its function toward the believers: guidance and mercy. The two verses together state the Qur'an's twofold office — corrective and salvific — and verse 77 adds the emphatic *inna* and *lām*: *and verily it is certainly a guidance and a mercy.*

**Hudan: Guidance as Direction and as Arrival**

*Hudā* is from *h-d-y*, guidance — the root analyzed at verse 2, where the Qur'an was described as *hudan* for the believers, and at verse 62, where God was named *the better of guides* (khayru'l-hādīn). The same root produced *ihdinā* at al-Fātiḥah 6, *yahdī* at verses 62–63, and *muhtadūn* at verse 63.

The tradition distinguished two senses of *hudā*, and the distinction governs verse 77:

*Hudā al-irshād wa'l-bayān* — the guidance of direction and clarification: showing the way. This is available to all and is the sense in which the Qur'an is guidance for mankind (2:185).

*Hudā al-tawfīq wa'l-ihlāl* — the guidance of enablement and arrival: bringing the one guided to the destination. This is God's alone and is granted to whom He wills.

> *"Verily thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guides whom He wills; and He knows best the guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

> *"And if thou art concerned for their guidance — verily God guides not whom He causes to stray."* (Qur'an 16:37)

Verse 77's *hudan* operates in both senses for the believer: the Qur'an shows him the way and, by God's enablement, brings him along it. Verse 80 will state the negative case — those who cannot be made to hear — and verse 81 the positive: *thou canst only make hear those who believe in Our signs and are submitters.*

**Raḥmatun: Mercy as the Effect of Guidance**

*"And a mercy."* *Raḥmah* is from *r-ḥ-m*, mercy, compassion — the root of *al-Raḥmān* and *al-Raḥīm*, which opened this sūrah at the basmalah and whose occurrence at verse 30 established it as the operative Name in the letter.

The coupling of *hudan* and *raḥmah* is one of the Qur'an's most characteristic pairings, and the source commentary lists the parallels where the coupling is applied to the Qur'an or the Torah: 6:154, 157; 7:203; 10:57; 16:64, 89.

> *"Then We gave Moses the Book, completing [Our favor] upon him who did good, and a detailed exposition of all things, and a guidance and a mercy (hudan wa raḥmatan), that haply they may believe in the meeting of their Lord."* (Qur'an 6:154)

> *"Say: 'It is, for those who believe, a guidance and a healing (hudan wa shifāʾ).'"* (Qur'an 41:44)

> *"O mankind! There has come unto you an admonition from your Lord, and a healing for what is in the breasts, and a guidance and a mercy for the believers."* (Qur'an 10:57)

> *"And We sent down not the Book unto thee save that thou mayest make clear unto them that wherein they differ, and as a guidance and a mercy (hudan wa raḥmatan) for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 16:64)

> *"And We have sent down unto thee the Reminder, that thou mayest make clear unto mankind what has been sent down unto them, and haply they may reflect."* (Qur'an 16:44)

> *"And We sent down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers (shifāʾun wa raḥmatun li'l-muʾminīn), and it increases the wrongdoers in naught but perdition."* (Qur'an 17:82)

The parallels establish a pattern: guidance, mercy, healing, clarification, admonition — a cluster of terms describing the Qur'an's effect, always with the qualification that the effect is realized in the believer.

Verse 17:82 supplies the pattern's negative pole, and it is essential to understanding verse 77: *it increases the wrongdoers in naught but perdition.* The same Book that is guidance and mercy to one person is perdition to another, and the difference is not in the Book. Verse 41:44 states this with complete explicitness:

> *"Say: 'It is, for those who believe, a guidance and a healing; and for those who believe not, in their ears is a heaviness, and it is a blindness for them. Those are called from a far place.'"* (Qur'an 41:44)

*Called from a far place* — the image of a man shouted at from a distance, who hears a sound and not a meaning — is the same image verses 80–81 will develop.

**Li'l-Muʾminīn: The Qualification**

*"For the believers."* The prepositional phrase restricts the effect, and the restriction is the verse's most important claim. The Qur'an is guidance and mercy *for those who believe*, not automatically for everyone who possesses it.

The restriction explains a fact every reader of the Qur'an encounters: two people read the same verse, and one is changed while the other is not. Verse 41:44 locates the difference in the ears and the heart of the hearer — *in their ears is a heaviness, and it is a blindness for them.* Verse 17:82 locates it in the moral condition — *the wrongdoers.* Verse 77 locates it in belief.

The commentators draw the inference that belief is not merely a prerequisite for benefiting from the Qur'an but a capacity that grows through it. Verse 8:2 states the mechanism:

> *"The believers are only those whose hearts are afraid when God is mentioned, and when His signs are recited unto them, they increase them in faith, and upon their Lord they rely."* (Qur'an 8:2)

*Yazīduhum īmānan* — "they increase them in faith" — describes the reciprocal relation: belief enables the hearing, and the hearing increases the belief. The cycle is the Qur'an's account of how a person becomes someone for whom the Book is guidance and mercy.

**The Coupling of the Two Terms**

Why *guidance and mercy* together rather than either alone? The commentators offer several reasons, and they converge.

**Guidance without mercy would be information.** A map is guidance; it tells you the road and does not care whether you walk it. The Qur'an is not a map — it is guidance accompanied by mercy, meaning that the One who shows the way also supplies the capacity to walk it, forgives the stumbles, and multiplies the small effort. Verse 73's *dhū faḍl* — Possessed of Bounty — is the theological ground of the mercy.

**Mercy without guidance would be sentiment.** A compassion that does not show the way leaves the person where he is. The Qur'an's mercy is directive: it tells him what to do, what to avoid, what to say, and where he is going. Verse 2's coupling — *a guidance and glad tidings for the believers* — states the same combination from the other side.

**The combination produces the Qur'an's characteristic tone.** The Book is neither a legal code nor a consolation; it is a guidance delivered in mercy, which is why it can command and console in the same verse, threaten and promise in the same passage, and move from the accounting of verses 89–90 to the assurance of verse 91 in a single breath.

Al-Naml's own structure demonstrates the combination. Verses 7–58 narrated prophets in mercy; verses 59–75 argued and warned; verse 77 names the whole as guidance and mercy; verses 82–93 will present the eschatological conclusion in which both operate.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 77 invites the reader to test the claim on himself, and the test is available immediately.

Does the Qur'an guide him? The question is not whether he can quote it but whether his next decision is different because of it — whether the verse he read this morning has any bearing on the transaction he will conduct this afternoon. Guidance that does not change the decision is not guidance in the Qur'an's sense; it is information.

Does it show him mercy? The question is whether reading it leaves him with hope or with despair, and the Qur'an's answer is that a reading producing despair has been misread: *Despair not of God's mercy; verily God forgives all sins* (39:53). Verse 53 is addressed to *those who have exceeded the limits against their own souls*, and it is one of the Qur'an's statements of mercy, and it is guidance — it tells the person what to do about his condition.

And is he among the believers? The question is the one verse 77 makes unavoidable, because the guidance and the mercy are qualified by the phrase. Verse 81 will answer it with a definition: *those who believe in Our signs and are submitters* — *bi-āyātinā yūqinūn* and *hum muslimūn*. Belief plus submission is the qualification, and both are available to the reader who wants them.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:78

> **Verily thy Lord shall judge between them by His Judgment, and He is the Mighty, the Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Judgment Deferred and Guaranteed**

*Inna rabbaka yaqḍī baynahum bi-ḥukmihī wa huwa'l-ʿAzīzu'l-ʿAlīm* — "Verily thy Lord judges between them by His Judgment, and He is the Mighty, the Knowing."

Verse 76 stated that the Qur'an narrates to the Children of Israel most of what they dispute. Verse 77 stated that it is guidance and mercy for the believers. Verse 78 states what happens to the dispute: God will judge between them.

The verse transfers the matter from narration to adjudication, and the transfer is a relief as well as a warning. The narrator is not required to convince the disputants; the Judge will decide between them. The Prophet ﷺ — addressed directly as *thy Lord* — is not the arbiter of Israel's disagreements; God is.

**Yaqḍī Baynahum: The Verb of Judicial Decision**

*Yaqḍī* is from *q-ḍ-y*, to decide, to judge, to decree, to accomplish, to pay — a root of wide judicial and executive range. Its cognates in the Qur'an include *qaḍāʾ*, decree, *qāḍī*, judge, and the verb's use for the completion of a matter:

> *"And when the prayer is completed (quḍiyati'l-ṣalāh), disperse in the land"* (Qur'an 62:10)

> *"So He decreed them (fa-qaḍāhunna) seven heavens in two days"* (Qur'an 41:12)

> *"And thy Lord has decreed (qaḍā) that you worship none but Him"* (Qur'an 17:23)

The verb at verse 78 is judicial in sense: God *decides between them*, issues the ruling in the case. The commentators note that the present tense *yaqḍī* carries a future meaning here — *shall judge* — as at verse 65's *yaʿlamu*, and the futurity is eschatological.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī explain that God will judge in the Hereafter about who is right, and al-Rāzī adds the qualification recorded in the source commentary: *it is not the duty of the Prophet to carry out this judgment.* The point is a limitation on prophetic office as much as a promise of divine justice. The Prophet ﷺ narrates, warns, and calls; he does not adjudicate the theological disputes of the People of the Book.

**The Qur'an's Formula of Deferred Judgment**

The verse belongs to a family of Qur'anic statements, and the source commentary lists them: God will, in the Hereafter, inform you of that wherein you differed — 2:113, 5:48, 6:164, 10:93, 16:39, 16:124, 22:69, 32:25, 39:3, 46.

> *"And the Jews say the Christians are upon nothing, and the Christians say the Jews are upon nothing, while they recite the Book. Even so say those who know not the like of their saying. And God will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein they differ."* (Qur'an 2:113)

> *"Unto thee We have sent down the Book in truth, confirming what was before it of the Book, and as a witness over it. So judge between them by what God has sent down, and follow not their caprice away from the truth that has come unto thee... And if God had willed, He would have made you one community; but [He willed] that He might try you in what He has given you. So vie with one another in good works. Unto God is your return, all together, and He will inform you of that wherein you differ."* (Qur'an 5:48)

> *"And God will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein they differ."* (Qur'an 22:69)

> *"And unto God is the return of all affairs."* — and: *"Verily thy Lord will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein they used to differ."* (Qur'an 32:25; 10:93; 16:124; 45:17)

Verse 2:113 is the most complete statement of the situation verse 78 addresses: the two communities each declare the other to be upon nothing, both recite the Book, and the outsiders say the same of both. The verse's conclusion is identical to verse 78's: *God will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection.*

Verse 5:48 is the most consequential for the community's conduct, because it draws the practical inference: *So vie with one another in good works* — *fastabiqū'l-khayrāt*. The dispute is not resolved by argument in this world; it is referred to the Day; and in the meantime the parties are instructed to compete in goodness rather than in refutation. That instruction is one of the Qur'an's most important statements about interreligious disagreement, and verse 78 is its al-Naml form.

**Bi-Ḥukmihī: "By His Judgment"**

The phrase *bi-ḥukmihī* — "by His judgment" — is unusual and the commentators discuss it. Al-Zamakhsharī reads it as meaning by a judgment that is His own, not derived from another's standard; al-Ṭabarī reads it as by His just decree; al-Qurṭubī notes that it emphasizes the independence and perfection of the judgment.

The genitive construction attaches the judgment to God personally, and the effect is to exclude every other standard. Human adjudication applies a law given by another; God's adjudication *is* the standard. The Qur'an states the exclusivity:

> *"Is it the judgment of the Age of Ignorance they seek? And who is better than God in judgment for a people who have certainty?"* (Qur'an 5:50)

> *"Such is God, your Lord. His is the creation and the command. Blessed be God, Lord of the Worlds."* (Qur'an 7:54)

> *"And unto God belongs the decision of the affair, all of it."* — and: *"The judgment belongs to none but God"* (Qur'an 6:57; 12:40, 67)

Verse 5:50's rhetorical question — *who is better than God in judgment?* — is qualified by *for a people who have certainty* (li-qawmin yūqinūn), and the qualification uses the same root as verse 81's *bi-āyātinā yūqinūn*: certainty. The person with certainty recognizes the superiority of God's judgment; the person without it prefers the judgment of ignorance.

**Al-ʿAzīzu'l-ʿAlīm: The Two Names Supporting the Judgment**

*"And He is the Mighty, the Knowing."* The pairing appears at the end of verses 9, 78, and 91 in this sūrah, and the source commentary notes its recurrence at 2:220, 228, 240; 3:6, 62; 5:118; 6:128.

*Al-ʿAzīz* — the Mighty, the Invincible, the Honorable: the Name guaranteeing that the judgment cannot be obstructed, appealed beyond, or evaded. Al-ʿIzzah is that which cannot be overcome.

*Al-ʿAlīm* — the Knowing: the Name guaranteeing that the judgment is informed. A mighty judge who does not know the facts judges arbitrarily; a knowing judge who cannot enforce is ineffective. The combination is the definition of just adjudication.

The two Names answer precisely the two problems of human judgment, which the Qur'an names at verse 20 in the case of Sheba's council — *we are people of might* (power without knowledge or justice) — and at verse 34 in the case of the entering kings — *they corrupt it* (power exercised without constraint). God's judgment is neither: it is *mighty* and *knowing*, and verse 78's assurance rests on both.

Verse 74 supplied the knowledge component in detail: the breasts' concealment and the disclosure are known. Verse 75 supplied the record: nothing hidden anywhere is outside the Book. Verse 78's *al-ʿAlīm* is therefore not an abstract claim but the conclusion of an established argument — the Judge knows because the interiors are known (74) and everything is recorded (75).

**The Verse's Function in the Sūrah's Arc**

Verse 78 closes a section, and its closure has a specific effect on the reader who has been following the dispute.

Verses 76–77 addressed the Qur'an's relation to the disagreement of the People of the Book. The reader — whether a seventh-century Makkan, a medieval Muslim, or a modern one — may reasonably ask: does the narration settle the matter? The answer verse 78 gives is no; the narration states the case and the adjudication is deferred to God.

The deferral is a discipline as much as a doctrine. It prevents the Muslim from claiming to have resolved the dispute by force of argument, and it prevents him from treating the opponent's rejection as a defeat of the revelation. The dispute is pending. The court has not sat. The evidence has been presented (vv. 76–77), the Judge is competent (v. 78), and the matter awaits the Day.

Verse 79 draws the practical conclusion from the deferral: *So trust thou in God; truly thou standest upon the manifest truth.* Since the judgment is not yours to execute, rely on the One who will.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 78 applies to every disagreement the reader cannot resolve, and the range is wider than interreligious dispute.

Within a family, a business, a community, or a scholarly field, there are disagreements in which both parties believe themselves right, in which the evidence is genuinely contested, and in which no arbitration is available. Verse 78 states that the arbitration exists and that it is competent, and it states this without resolving the disagreement in the meantime.

The instruction the verse implies is the one verse 5:48 made explicit: refer the matter to God and compete in good works. The Muslim tradition built a substantial jurisprudence on this principle — the etiquette of disagreement (*ādāb al-ikhtilāf*) — which holds that disagreement on matters where evidence permits multiple readings is legitimate, that the disputant's integrity is not impeached by his position, that the matter belongs to God, and that the practical duty in the interim is *fastabiqū'l-khayrāt*.

Verse 78's Names close the application. The Judge is *Mighty*, so the matter cannot be taken from Him by power; and *Knowing*, so no hidden evidence — no concealed breast, no unrecorded act — will be omitted from His consideration. The person who has been wronged in a dispute, or who knows he is right and cannot prove it, or who suspects he is wrong and cannot determine it, has in verse 78 a court that will hear the case properly. That is the verse's comfort, and it is also its warning: the same competence applies when he is the wrongdoer.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:79

> **So trust in God; truly thou standest upon the manifest truth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command of Reliance**

*Fa-tawakkal ʿalā'Llāh; innaka ʿalā'l-ḥaqqi'l-mubīn* — "So rely upon God; verily thou art upon the manifest truth."

The verse begins with the *fāʾ* of consequence — "so, therefore" — connecting it to what precedes. Because God judges between them by His judgment (v. 78), *therefore rely upon God.* The reliance is the response to the deferral of judgment: the matter is not yours, so place it with the One whose it is.

The verse is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in the singular, and the source commentary lists the parallels for the virtue of reliance: 3:122, 160; 5:11; 9:51; 12:67; 14:11–12; 39:38; 58:10; 64:13.

**Tawakkul: The Doctrine of Reliance**

*Tawakkul* is Form V from *w-k-l*, to entrust, to delegate, to rely — the same root as *wakīl*, guardian, trustee, agent, and *tawakkala*, to entrust oneself. The Qur'an uses *wakīl* of God repeatedly:

> *"God is Knower of all things"* — and: *"And upon God let the believers rely (wa ʿalā Llāhi falyatawakkali'l-muʾminūn)"* (Qur'an 3:122, 160; 5:11; 8:2, 49; 9:51; 14:11; 58:10; 64:13)

> *"And unto God belongs the Unseen of the heavens and the earth, and unto Him is returned the affair, all of it. So worship Him and rely upon Him (fa-tawakkal ʿalayhi); and thy Lord is not heedless of what you do."* (Qur'an 11:123)

> *"Sufficient for us is God, and an excellent Trustee (niʿma'l-wakīl)."* (Qur'an 3:173)

> *"And He is the Forgiving, the Loving, Possessor of the Glorious Throne, the Doer of what He wills"* — and: *"And rely upon the Mighty, the Merciful (wa tawakkal ʿalā'l-ʿAzīzi'l-Raḥīm)"* (Qur'an 26:217)

Verse 11:123 is the closest structural parallel to verse 79 and is widely regarded as the last verse revealed; it combines worship, reliance, and the assurance of divine awareness, exactly as verse 79 combines reliance with the assurance of standing on the manifest truth.

Verse 26:217's addition of the two Names — *the Mighty, the Merciful* — supplies the theology of reliance: the One relied upon is powerful enough to act and merciful enough to act well. Verse 78's *al-ʿAzīzu'l-ʿAlīm* and verse 79's *fa-tawakkal* form the same pair.

**The Tradition on Tawakkul**

The ḥadīth literature develops the doctrine extensively, and several reports are central.

> *"If you relied upon God with the reliance He deserves, He would provide for you as He provides for the birds: they go out in the morning with empty stomachs and return in the evening full."* (al-Tirmidhī 2344; Ibn Mājah 4164; Aḥmad, graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

The report is the tradition's standard proof that reliance does not exclude action — the birds *go out*. Al-Qushayrī and Ibn Rajab both build on this point: *tawakkul* is the heart's reliance with the body's effort, and the abandonment of effort is not reliance but incapacity.

> *"O God, there is no good but the good of the Hereafter"* — and the report in al-Bukhārī 3: *"Whoever would be the strongest of people, let him rely upon God; and whoever would be the wealthiest, let him be confident in what is with God rather than what is in his hand."*

> *"The seventy thousand who will enter the Garden without reckoning... they are those who do not seek ruqyah, do not take omens, do not cauterize, and upon their Lord they rely."* (al-Bukhārī 5705; Muslim 218)

The last report names *tawakkul* as the defining quality of the seventy thousand, and its list of excluded practices — augury, cautery as a superstitious resort, and the seeking of incantation — is precisely the territory of verse 47's *al-ṭāʾir* and verse 76's *ghayb*. The reliance that defines the elect is reliance on God in place of omens.
