> *"When thou leavest thy house for prayer, say: 'In the Name of God, I rely upon God; there is no power and no might save with God.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 5095; al-Tirmidhī 3426, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**ʿAlā'l-Ḥaqqi'l-Mubīn: "Upon the Manifest Truth"**

The source commentary notes the range: *thou standest upon* could also be rendered "thou followest" or "thou art based upon." The preposition *ʿalā* with the verb of being produces the image of standing, basing, or being established upon something — a foundation rather than a path, though both readings are held.

*Al-ḥaqq al-mubīn* — "the manifest truth," or "the clear truth" — combines the two terms the sūrah has used throughout: *ḥaqq*, truth, reality, right (vv. 15, 79; cf. *bi'l-ḥaqq* at 64), and *mubīn*, clear, manifest (vv. 1, 75, 79).

The Qur'an uses the phrase repeatedly:

> *"That is God, your Lord, the Truth (al-Ḥaqq). What then is there after the truth save error?"* (Qur'an 10:32)

> *"So the truth was established (fa-ḥaqqa'l-ḥaqq), and what they used to do became vain."* (Qur'an 7:118)

> *"And say: 'The truth has come from your Lord; so whoever wills, let him believe, and whoever wills, let him disbelieve.'"* (Qur'an 18:29)

> *"This is the narration in truth (bi'l-ḥaqq)."* — cf. 3:62

Verse 10:32's rhetorical question — *what then is there after the truth save error?* — states the binary that verse 79 assumes: the Prophet ﷺ is *upon* the truth, and there is no third position. The reliance commanded is therefore not reliance in uncertainty but reliance from a known foundation.

**Why the Assurance Precedes the Difficulty**

Verse 79 stands between verse 78's deferred judgment and verses 80–81's declaration of the Prophet's ﷺ inability to make the dead hear. The placement is deliberate and al-Rāzī's interpretation of verses 80–81 explains it.

Al-Rāzī holds — as the source commentary records — that these verses help the Prophet's ﷺ heart to actualize reliance upon God and to become stronger thereby, arguing that when one hopes to get something from others, it weakens one's ability to manifest opposition to them; by absolving the Prophet ﷺ of any responsibility or power over whether his people believe or disbelieve, God strengthens his ability to declare and teach the truth.

The analysis is psychologically precise and it has a general application. The person who needs something from others cannot speak freely to them. The preacher who needs his audience's approval softens his message; the teacher who needs his students' regard moderates his standards; the advisor who needs his client's business dilutes his counsel. The dependence produces the distortion.

Verse 79's command therefore arrives with its enabling clause: *rely upon God* — because *thou art upon the manifest truth.* The reliance removes the need, and the removal of the need makes free speech possible. Verses 80–81 then complete the process by declaring that the audience's response is not in the Prophet's ﷺ power at all: the dead cannot be made to hear, and only those who believe and submit can be made to hear.

The sequence is: rely on God (79) → you are on the truth (79) → you cannot make them hear anyway (80) → your only audience is those already inclined (81) → therefore speak the truth without needing their response (82ff.).

**Tawakkul and the Limits of Responsibility**

The verse's deeper content is a redefinition of what the Prophet ﷺ is responsible for, and the redefinition is the Qur'an's consistent teaching:

> *"And if they turn away — thy duty is only the clear proclamation"* — and: *"Remind; thou art but a reminder. Thou art not a controller over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

> *"Not upon thee is their guidance; but God guides whom He wills."* (Qur'an 2:272)

> *"And verily We know that thy breast is constricted by what they say. So glorify with the praise of thy Lord and be among those who prostrate. And worship thy Lord until certainty comes unto thee."* (Qur'an 15:97–99)

Verse 15:97–99 was quoted at verse 70 of this sūrah as the remedy for the distress produced by the plotters, and verse 79 supplies the complementary instruction: the distress is relieved by reliance, and reliance is possible because the standing is on truth.

The redefinition has a consequence for every person who teaches, advises, or calls. He is responsible for the delivery, not for the reception. Verse 5:99 states it in the strongest form:

> *"The Messenger's duty is only the proclamation (al-balāgh); and God knows what you disclose and what you conceal."* (Qur'an 5:99)

Verse 74 said the same thing about the interiors — God knows the concealed and the disclosed — and verse 5:99 draws the inference for the messenger: since God knows and judges, the messenger's task is only to convey. Verse 79's *fa-tawakkal* is the emotional and spiritual form of that inference.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 79 is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and applies, in the tradition's reading, to everyone in his position — which is to say, everyone who must state a truth to people whose response he cannot control.

The application has three parts.

**Rely upon God rather than upon the outcome.** The outcome is not in the reader's hand; verses 80–81 will say so explicitly. What is in his hand is the statement of the truth and the reliance while stating it. The practical test is whether his message changes depending on who is listening — and if it does, the reliance is not yet where verse 79 places it.

**Know what you are standing on.** Verse 79's assurance is not a general encouragement; it is a specific claim about the Prophet's ﷺ foundation, and its application requires the reader to know his own. A person who states a position without knowing its basis cannot rely on God with respect to it, because there is nothing to rely upon God for. Verse 64's demand for a proof — *bring your proof if you are truthful* — is the discipline that establishes the foundation, and verse 79 is the reliance that follows it.

**Accept the limits of your audience.** The dead will not hear (v. 80). The deaf who have turned their backs will not hear (v. 80). The blind in their error will not be guided (v. 81). The people who will hear are those who already believe in the signs and submit (v. 81). A person who has internalized this will stop measuring his effectiveness by the resistance of the unwilling and will direct his effort where verse 81 locates the reception.

That is the verse's practical wisdom, and it is why al-Rāzī read the sequence as strengthening rather than discouraging. The command to rely is given at the point where human influence ends, and the point where human influence ends is the point where reliance begins.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:80

> **Surely thou dost not make the dead hear; nor dost thou make the deaf hear the call when they turn their backs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First of Two Declarations of Limits**

*Innaka lā tusmiʿu'l-mawtā wa lā tusmiʿu'l-ṣumma'l-duʿāʾa idhā wallaw mudbirīn* — "Verily thou dost not make the dead hear, nor dost thou make the deaf hear the call when they have turned away, retreating."

Verse 79 commanded reliance and affirmed the manifest truth. Verse 80 begins the explanation of why reliance is necessary: because the outcome of the call is not in the caller's power. The verse uses two images — the dead and the deaf — and both are drawn from the sūrah's own established vocabulary.

**Lā Tusmiʿu: The Verb of Making Hear**

*Tusmiʿu* is Form IV from *s-m-ʿ*, hearing — the causative of *samiʿa*, to hear. Form IV *asmā* means to make hear, to cause to hear, to get someone to listen. The distinction between the two forms is the verse's grammatical core: hearing is one thing; *making* someone hear is another, and the second is what the Prophet ﷺ cannot do.

The Qur'an uses both forms of the same root extensively:

> *"Verily thou makest not the dead to hear"* (Qur'an 27:80)

> *"Verily thou canst not make the dead hear, nor make the deaf hear the call when they have turned their backs, retreating."* (Qur'an 30:52)

> *"And thou art not one who makes those in the graves hear"* (Qur'an 35:22)

> *"And among them are those who listen unto thee. Canst thou make the deaf hear, even though they do not understand?"* (Qur'an 10:42)

Verse 30:52 is nearly identical to verse 80 and is generally taken as its parallel — both in the sense of wording and of context, since both appear in the latter part of Makkah's sūrahs. Verse 35:22 extends the image to the graves, which is the Qur'an's most explicit statement of the case.

**Al-Mawtā: The Dead**

*"The dead."* The word is the plural of *mayyit*, dead, from *m-w-t*, death. Verse 22 used the same root in the phrase *ghayra hayyin wa lā mayyit* — "neither living nor dead" — and verse 67 used *amwatan* — "dead" — in the dust objection: *"we were dust, and our fathers, shall we indeed be brought forth?"*

The image here is spiritual rather than physical, and the commentators are unanimous on the point. Al-Ṭabarī explains that those whose hearts God has sealed cannot be made to understand the truth, and those God has made deaf cannot then be made to hear. The "dead" are those whose hearts are dead:

> *"Or he who was dead and We gave him life, and We made for him a light by which he walks among the people — is he like him whose likeness is in the darknesses, from which he does not emerge?"* (Qur'an 6:122)

> *"And those who disbelieve — their deeds are as a mirage in a desert..."* — and: *"The likeness of the two parties is as the blind and the deaf, and the seeing and the hearing. Are they equal in similitude?"* (Qur'an 11:24)

Verse 6:122 supplies the counterpart to verse 80: God gives life to the dead heart, and the giving is His act, not the caller's. Verse 22:46, quoted by the source commentary, states the location of the death:

> *"Have they not journeyed upon the earth, that they might have hearts by which to understand or ears by which to hear? Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

The blindness is in the hearts *within the breasts* — the same *ṣudūr* of verse 74. The interior that God knows (v. 74) is the interior that can be dead (v. 80), and the two verses are connected by the location.

**Al-Ṣumma: The Deaf**

*"Nor the deaf."* *Ṣumm* is the plural of *aṣamm*, deaf, from *ṣ-m-m*, deafness, and the same root as *ṭarsha*, though the Qur'an's word is *ṣ-m-m*. The Qur'an's use of deafness for spiritual incapacity is one of its most persistent images:

> *"Deaf (ṣumm), dumb (bukm), blind (ʿumy) — so they will not return."* (Qur'an 2:18)

> *"And We have made upon their hearts coverings lest they understand it, and in their ears a heaviness (waqran)"* (Qur'an 6:25; 17:46; 18:57; 41:5)

> *"And the likeness of those who disbelieve is as the likeness of one who shouts unto that which hears nothing but a call and a cry — deaf, dumb, blind; so they understand not."* (Qur'an 2:171)

Verse 2:171 is the most vivid of the parallels: the disbeliever is like an animal that hears the herder's shout and perceives only sound, not meaning. The Qur'an's diagnosis is not that the organ fails but that the perception does not occur — the sound arrives and the meaning does not.

Verse 6:25's *waqran* — a heaviness, a load in the ears — is the same image, and the tradition connected it to the sealing of verse 66: what God has sealed cannot be opened by human effort.

**Al-Duʿāʾa: The Call**

*"The call."* *Duʿāʾ* is from *d-ʿ-w*, to call, to summon, to invoke — the root of *daʿwah*, the call to God, and *duʿāʾ*, supplication. The verse specifies what the deaf are not made to hear: *the call*, the proclamation, the invitation.

The specification matters because it identifies the genre of the Prophet's ﷺ activity. He is not delivering information to be decoded but a *call* to be answered, and a call has a structure: it summons, it addresses, it requires response. The deaf man who does not hear the call is not merely uninformed; he is unsummoned, and his unsummoned state is his own condition rather than a defect in the call.

The Qur'an's use of *duʿāʾ* for its own proclamation is standard:

> *"O you who believe! Respond to God and to the Messenger when he calls you to that which gives you life"* (Qur'an 8:24)

> *"And God calls unto the Abode of Peace, and guides whom He wills unto a straight path"* (Qur'an 10:25)

> *"Say: 'This is my way. I call unto God (adʿū ilā Llāh) upon insight, I and those who follow me'"* (Qur'an 12:108)

Verse 8:24 supplies the essential qualification: the call is *to that which gives you life* — and the one who does not answer remains dead. Verse 80's dead and deaf are therefore those who have not answered the call to life, and their condition is described in the terms of what they refused.

**Idhā Wallaw Mudbirīn: "When They Have Turned Away, Retreating"**

The clause adds movement to the image and intensifies it. *Wallaw* is from *w-l-y*, to turn, to depart; *mudbirīn* is the active plural participle of *adbara*, Form IV of *d-b-r*, to be behind, to turn the back — the same root as *dubur*, the back, and *idbār*, retreat.

The construction *wallaw mudbirīn* — "they turned, retreating" — uses a circumstantial qualifier (*ḥāl*) that describes the manner of the turning: they turned *in a state of retreat*. The image is of a person who has not merely turned aside but is moving away, actively, with his back presented.

The Qur'an uses the same verb pair:

> *"And when they turned away (tawallaw), God turned their hearts away"* (Qur'an 61:5)

> *"That is because they said to those who hated what God sent down: 'We will obey you in part of the affair.' And God knows their secrets"* — and: *"And when they turned away"* (Qur'an 47:25–27)

> *"And they say: 'We believe in God and the Messenger, and we obey'; then a party among them turns away (tawallā) after that; and those are not believers."* (Qur'an 24:47)

Verse 61:5 states the principle that explains verse 80: *when they turned away, God turned their hearts away.* The turning is the person's; the confirmation of the turning is God's. Verse 80 describes the result — a man whose back is presented and whose ears are closed — and the description is of a condition the man entered by turning.

**The Verse's Logic: Why the Caller Cannot Make Hear**

Verse 80 makes a claim about the limits of prophetic influence, and its logic is worth stating explicitly because it is one of the Qur'an's clearest accounts of the mechanics of belief.

Hearing in the Qur'an's sense is not acoustic reception but responsive perception. A man can receive the sound and not perceive the meaning (2:171); he can perceive the meaning and not respond; he can respond and not act. The full act — hearing that produces faith — requires the operation of God in the heart, because the heart is where the response occurs and the heart is not accessible to another human being.

The Prophet ﷺ can therefore deliver the call. He cannot produce the hearing. Verse 28:56 states the same limit from God's side:

> *"Verily thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guides whom He wills; and He knows best the guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

The verse was revealed, in the standard report, concerning Abū Ṭālib, the Prophet's ﷺ uncle, whom he loved and wished to believe; and the report at al-Bukhārī 4772 and Muslim 24 records the deathbed scene in which the Prophet ﷺ urged him to say *lā ilāha illa'Llāh* and Abū Ṭālib died on the religion of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. The verse descended after, and its statement — *thou guidest not whom thou lovest* — is the definitive limit on prophetic power.

Verse 80 is the same limit stated as an inability to make hear. The two verses are the Qur'an's paired declarations that the outcome of the call belongs to God, and they occur in adjacent sūrahs (27 and 28) in similar positions.

**The Verse's Comfort**

The declaration sounds like a rebuke and functions as a comfort. The caller who has delivered the message and been rejected is not a failure; he has done what he was sent to do. The resistance he encountered was not a measure of his delivery but of the condition of the recipient.

The Qur'an states the consolation explicitly at verse 70 of this sūrah — *grieve not over them* — and at 35:8:

> *"Then wilt thou destroy thyself with grief over them, if they believe not in this discourse, out of sorrow?"* (Qur'an 18:6)

> *"Haply thou wilt destroy thyself (bākhiʿun nafsaka) that they believe not."* (Qur'an 26:3)

> *"And if it is heavy upon thee their turning away — then, if thou art able to seek a tunnel in the earth or a ladder in the heaven, that thou mayest bring them a sign... And if God had willed, He would have gathered them upon guidance. So be not among the ignorant."* (Qur'an 6:35)

Verse 18:6's *bākhiʿ* — destroying oneself with grief — is the condition verse 80 addresses, and the address is therapeutic. The Prophet ﷺ is told what he cannot do in order to release him from the grief of trying.

Verse 6:35 completes the therapy by supplying the counterfactual: if God had willed, He would have gathered them upon guidance. The non-belief of the audience is not evidence of the caller's failure; it is evidence of a divine will operating on a scale the caller cannot see. That is verse 79's *fa-tawakkal* — reliance — in its doctrinal ground.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:81

> **Nor canst thou guide the blind away from their error. Thou canst only make hear those who believe in Our signs and are submitters.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Limitation and the Definition of the Audience**

*Wa mā anta bi-hādi'l-ʿumyi ʿan ḍalālatihim; in tusmiʿu illā man yuʾminu bi-āyātinā fa-hum muslimūn* — "And thou art not the guider of the blind away from their error. Thou makest hear only him who believes in Our signs, and they are submitters."

Verse 80 declared two incapacities — making the dead hear, making the deaf hear. Verse 81 declares a third — guiding the blind — and then states, in the second sentence, who *can* be made to hear. The verse therefore contains both the limit and the audience, and the audience's definition is one of the Qur'an's most concise statements of what a believer is.

**Wa Mā Anta Bi-Hādī: The Negation with Bāʾ**

The construction *mā anta bi-* — "thou art not one who..." — is a strengthened negation using the *bāʾ* of confirmation, and it is more emphatic than simple *mā anta hādī*. The *bāʾ* construction negates the *identity* rather than the action: not merely "thou dost not guide" but "thou art not a guider of the blind."

The Qur'an uses the construction elsewhere:

> *"Verily thou canst not guide whom thou lovest"* — and: *"And thou art not (mā anta) a warner save..."* — cf. 13:7; 35:23

> *"And thou art but a warner"* — and: *"Thou art not (lasta) upon them a controller"* (Qur'an 88:22)

> *"Not upon thee is their guidance"* (Qur'an 2:272)

Verse 13:7 supplies the parallel statement in the strongest terms:

> *"And those who disbelieve say: 'Why has a sign not been sent down unto him from his Lord?' Thou art but a warner (innamā anta mundhir), and for every people there is a guide."* (Qur'an 13:7)

*Innamā anta mundhir* — "thou art only a warner" — is the limitation stated as identity, and verse 81's *mā anta bi-hādi'l-ʿumy* is its negative form. The two together define the office: warner, not guider; deliverer, not determiner.

**Al-ʿUmyi ʿAn Ḍalālatihim: The Blind, Away From Their Error**

*"The blind, away from their error."* *ʿUmy* is the plural of *aʿmā*, blind, from *ʿ-m-y*, blindness — the same root as *ʿamūn* at verse 66, where the sūrah described the objectors as *"in darkness, wandering, blind."* The word reappears here as the third image in the sequence dead–deaf–blind, and the sequence is the Qur'an's standard triad:

> *"Deaf, dumb, blind"* (Qur'an 2:18; 2:171)

> *"And We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces — blind, dumb, deaf"* (Qur'an 17:97)

> *"And whoever is blind in this [world] will be blind in the Hereafter, and further astray in path"* (Qur'an 17:72)

Verse 17:72 is the verse that establishes the continuity between the two blindnesses: the blindness of this world persists into the next. Verse 81's *ʿan ḍalālatihim* — "away from their error" — uses *ḍalālah*, the state of being astray, from *ḍ-l-l*, the same root as *ḍāllīn* at al-Fātiḥah 7 and *yaḍillūna* at verse 63 of this sūrah.

The preposition *ʿan* — "away from" — specifies the direction of the guidance sought: not toward something but *away from* the error they are in. The image is of a man walking in a wrong direction whom one would need to turn around, and the verse says the Prophet ﷺ cannot turn him. Verse 22:46, quoted in the source commentary, explains why:

> *"Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

The blindness is not in the organ that can be shown a sight; it is in the heart, which cannot be reached by another person's effort.

**In Tusmiʿu Illā Man Yūminu Bi-Āyātinā: The Definition of the Audience**

*"Thou makest hear only him who believes in Our signs."* The construction *in ... illā* — "not ... except" — is the same restrictive form used at verse 65 (*lā yaʿlamu man fī'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi'l-ghayba illa'Llāh*), and it produces exclusivity: no one is made to hear except the one described.

The description is *man yuʾminu bi-āyātinā* — "he who believes in Our signs." The phrase is crucial and the commentators have discussed its apparent circularity: how can the Prophet ﷺ make hear only those who already believe, when the making-hear is what produces belief?

The answers converge on a distinction between two stages of belief. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the one who believes in the signs is the one whose heart is open to them — disposed, prepared, inclined — as distinct from the one who has already attained complete faith. Al-Qurṭubī reads the verse as describing those who accept the signs when they come and are therefore reachable. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both note that the belief referred to is the readiness to believe, the disposition that makes hearing possible.

The reading is confirmed by the Qur'an's parallel usage:

> *"And warn thy nearest kin"* — and: *"Verily thou art a warner, and We have sent thee with the truth as a bearer of glad tidings and a warner"* (Qur'an 26:214; 35:24)

> *"And remind; for the reminder benefits the believers (fa-dhakkir fa-inna'l-dhikrā tanfaʿu'l-muʾminīn)."* (Qur'an 51:55)

Verse 51:55 states the same principle from the positive side: reminding *benefits the believers.* The reminder is delivered to all; it benefits those whose hearts are disposed to receive it. Verse 81 is the identical claim in the idiom of hearing.

Verse 2:26 gives the clearest parallel:

> *"Verily God is not ashamed to strike a similitude of a gnat or of what is above it. As for those who believe, they know that it is the truth from their Lord; and as for those who disbelieve, they say: 'What does God intend by this similitude?' He causes many to stray thereby, and He guides many thereby; and He causes to stray thereby none but the dissolute."* (Qur'an 2:26)

The same sign produces knowledge in one group and objection in another. Verse 17:82 states the general law: *We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers, and it increases the wrongdoers in naught but perdition.* The audience is defined not by the delivery but by the disposition.

**Fa-Hum Muslimūn: "And They Are Submitters"**

The final clause completes the definition. *Muslimūn* is the active plural participle of *aslama*, Form IV of *s-l-m*, to submit, to surrender, to enter into peace — the same root as *islām* and *salām*. The *faʾ* of the clause is consecutive or explanatory: *they believe in Our signs, and [therefore, and it follows that] they are submitters.*

The conjunction of belief and submission is the Qur'an's definition of the Muslim, and verse 81 supplies it in its most compact form: *those who believe in Our signs and are submitters.*

Belief without submission is knowledge that does not act; submission without belief is action without foundation. Verse 49:14 draws the distinction with unusual sharpness:

> *"The bedouins say: 'We believe.' Say: 'You believe not; but say: "We have submitted (aslamnā)" — for faith has not yet entered your hearts. And if you obey God and His Messenger, He will not diminish aught of your deeds. Verily God is Forgiving, Merciful.'"* (Qur'an 49:14)

The verse distinguishes *islām* as outward submission from *īmān* as inward faith, and states that the second had not yet entered the hearts of the first group. Verse 81 joins them: the audience of the Prophet's ﷺ hearing is the one who *believes in the signs* and *is a submitter* — the two together.

The ḥadīth of Gabriel, recorded at al-Bukhārī 50 and Muslim 8 and quoted at verse 65, gives the same conjunction from the other direction: it defines *islām* as the five acts, *īmān* as the six articles, and *iḥsān* as worshipping God as though seeing Him. The three are one religion (*al-dīn*), and verse 81's two terms are the first two of the three.

**The Verse's Application: Finding One's Audience**

Verse 81 has a practical use that is unusual in the Qur'an's declarative verses: it tells the caller where his effort will land.

**Not on the dead.** The person whose heart is sealed, who has decided, who has turned his back — verse 80 says the call will not reach him. The effort expended there is not wasted in the sense of being unrewarded for the caller (verse 5:99 makes the delivery itself the duty), but it will not produce hearing.

**Not on the deaf who have turned.** The person who has heard and walked away, who presents his back, who has closed the matter — the call will not reopen it. Verse 61:5's *when they turned away, God turned their hearts away* describes the mechanism.

**Not on the blind in their error.** The person whose error is a matter of the heart's blindness, not of insufficient information — more argument will not turn him, because argument addresses the eyes and the blindness is in the breast.

**Yes on those who believe in the signs and submit.** The person whose heart is disposed, who accepts the sign when it comes, whose acceptance produces action — the call reaches him, and the reaching is the Prophet's ﷺ hearing.

The application for the reader is a discipline of allocation. His time, his patience, his eloquence, and his hope are limited, and verse 81 tells him where they will produce hearing. The tradition developed the point in the etiquette of counsel: advise the one who asks, teach the one who seeks, warn the one who listens, and leave the one who has turned. The Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said:

> *"The wise man's counsel is not given to one who does not seek it."* — and: *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

The second report is the practical form of verse 81 for ordinary speech: the person who has nothing receptive in front of him should be silent, and the person who has a receptive listener should speak.

Verse 81 also supplies a comfort that verse 80 only implied. The caller's audience is not empty. *Those who believe in Our signs and are submitters* exist, and they are reachable, and the reaching is real. The sūrah's own structure demonstrates the claim: verses 15–44 narrated David, Solomon, and the hoopoe — the grateful few of verse 34:13 — and verse 77 named the believers for whom the Qur'an is guidance and mercy. Verse 81 identifies them as the audience, and verse 82 will begin the eschatological conclusion addressed to them and to everyone else.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:82

> **And when the Word comes upon them, We shall bring forth for them a beast from the earth who will speak to them of how mankind was not certain of Our signs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Beast**

*Wa idhā waqaʿa'l-qawlu ʿalayhim akhrajnā lahum dābbatan mina'l-arḍi tukallimuhum anna'l-nāsa kānū bi-āyātinā lā yūqinūn* — "And when the Word falls upon them, We shall bring forth for them a beast from the earth which will speak to them, because mankind was not certain of Our signs."

Verse 82 begins the sūrah's eschatological conclusion, and it begins with the single most enigmatic image in al-Naml: a beast brought forth from the earth that speaks to human beings. The verse has generated more speculative literature than any other in the sūrah, and the discipline of reading it correctly is as important as its content.

**Idhā Waqaʿa'l-Qawlu ʿAlayhim: When the Word Falls**

*"When the Word comes upon them."* *Waqaʿa* is from *w-q-ʿ*, to fall, to occur, to befall, to alight — the verb of an event's arrival, as a bird alights or a blow falls. *Al-qawl* — the Word — is the divine decree or pronouncement.

The source commentary records two principal readings. Some connect the phrase with similar language about the Word "coming due" — 10:96, 28:63, 36:7, 70, 39:71, 40:6 — which connotes the coming of Divine Judgment, and al-Ṭabarī refers to 10:33 and 36:7. For others the Word signifies that it is necessary for God's Wrath to come upon them (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī).

The parallels:

> *"Thus has the Word of thy Lord come due (ḥaqqat kalimatu rabbika) upon those who do wrong, because they were dissolute, that they will not believe."* (Qur'an 10:33)

> *"And the Word has come due upon them (wa waqaʿa'l-qawlu ʿalayhim) for their having done wrong, so they do not speak."* (Qur'an 27:85 — this sūrah, three verses later)

> *"And the Word came due upon them (wa waqaʿat al-kalimah ʿalayhim) for their having done wrong, so they do not speak."* — cf. 27:85

> *"Verily those against whom the Word of thy Lord has come due (ḥaqqat ʿalayhim kalimatu rabbika) will not believe, even if every sign comes unto them, until they see the painful punishment."* (Qur'an 10:96–97)

> *"And the Word came due upon them (wa ḥaqqat al-kalimah) for their having done wrong, so they do not speak"* — cf. 28:63; 36:7, 70; 39:71; 40:6

> *"And the Word shall fall upon them (waqaʿa'l-qawl) for what they wronged, so they will not speak."* (Qur'an 27:85)

Verse 10:96–97 is the most explicit statement of the condition: the Word has come due on a people, and they *will not believe even if every sign comes unto them*, until they see the punishment. Verse 82 describes the arrival of such a sign — the beast — at a time when the Word has already fallen.

Verse 36:7 supplies the parallel in the strongest form:

> *"And the Word has come due upon most of them (fa-ḥaqqa'l-qawlu ʿalā aktharihim), so they do not believe."* (Qur'an 36:7)

The sequence in verse 36 is the same as in verse 82: the Word falls, belief becomes impossible, and a sign arrives too late to help.

**The Sign That Comes After the Closing**

The tradition's central interpretive principle for verse 82 is that the beast is one of the *major signs of the Hour* (al-ʿalāmāt al-kubrā), and that it arrives at the point after which belief no longer benefits. The source commentary quotes the ḥadīth:

> *"There are three things that, when they come, no soul's belief will avail it, if they did not believe before, or if their belief earned them no good: the rising of the sun from the west, the Dajjāl, and the beast of the earth."* (Muslim 158; al-Tirmidhī 3072, from Abū Hurayrah)

The report is the key to the verse. It groups the beast with the sun's rising from the west and the Dajjāl as three events that close the door of beneficial belief, and it cites the principle from 6:158:

> *"On the day some of the signs of thy Lord come, no soul's belief will avail it, if it believed not before, or earned good in its belief."* (Qur'an 6:158)

Verse 6:158 was quoted at verse 71 in connection with the demand for a date, and it supplies the theological frame for verse 82: there is a point after which signs no longer produce faith, and the beast is one of the signs that marks or follows that point.

Muslim 158's fuller text lists the ten major signs: the smoke, the Dajjāl, the beast, the rising of the sun from the west, the descent of Jesus son of Mary, Yaʾjūj and Maʾjūj, and three landslides — one in the east, one in the west, and one in the Arabian peninsula — the last of which is a fire emerging from Yemen driving people to their place of gathering. The beast is third in the list, and its position among the ten is the reason the verse is read eschatologically.

**Dābbah: The Word for Beast**

*"A beast."* *Dābbah* is from *d-b-b*, to creep, to crawl, to walk with short steps — the same root as *dabīb*, creeping movement, and *dabb*, a slow walk. The source commentary gives the lexical range precisely: a word whose most general meaning is anything that crawls or walks upon the earth, including human beings; it can often have the special sense of an animal that serves as a mount, but it can also include insects and sea animals that crawl.

The Qur'an uses *dābbah* extensively:

> *"And there is no creature (dābbah) crawling on the earth but that its provision is upon God; and He knows its dwelling place and its place of deposit. All is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 11:6)

> *"And God created every creature (dābbah) from water; and among them are those who creep upon their bellies, and among them those who walk on two, and among them those who walk on four. God creates what He wills."* (Qur'an 24:45)

> *"And there is no creature on the earth, nor a bird that flies on its wings, but that they are communities like you. We have neglected nothing in the Book; then unto their Lord they shall be gathered."* (Qur'an 6:38)

> *"And whatever creature (dābbah) is in the heavens and the earth prostrates to God"* (Qur'an 16:49)

> *"And of His signs is the creation of the heavens and the earth and what He has scattered therein of creatures (dābbah); and He is over gathering them, when He wills, Able."* (Qur'an 42:29)

Verse 42:29 is the closest thematic parallel to verse 82 and is worth noting: God has scattered *dābbah* in the heavens and earth, and *He is over gathering them, when He wills, Able* — *wa huwa ʿalā jamʿihim idhā yashāʾu qadīr*. The gathering of the creatures is a stated divine capability, and verse 82 is one instance of its exercise.

Verse 24:45's inclusion of human beings within *dābbah* is significant for interpretation: the word does not exclude a human being, and the source commentary records that some say the beast will actually be a human being (al-Qurṭubī), though this is not a widely held position.

Verse 6:38 adds a further dimension: *they are communities like you* — *umamun amthālukum* — and *unto their Lord they shall be gathered.* The gathering of the creatures is eschatological, and a tradition reported at Abū Dāwūd 1738 and Aḥmad holds that the animals will be gathered and requited, even the hornless sheep taking its due from the horned.

**Min Al-Arḍ: From the Earth**

*"From the earth."* The origin is specified, and the specification connects the beast to the earth from which humanity itself came:

> *"And from it We created you, and into it We return you, and from it We shall bring you forth another time."* (Qur'an 20:55)

> *"And God caused you to grow from the earth [as] a growth"* (Qur'an 71:17)

Verse 67 of this sūrah quoted the objectors: *"when we are dust (turāban), shall we indeed be brought forth?"* — and the dust is *turāb*, earth. The beast brought forth *from the earth* is thus brought from the same material the objectors dismissed, and its emergence is a demonstration that the earth does yield what God commands it to yield.

**Tukallimuhum: "It Will Speak to Them"**

*"Who will speak to them."* *Tukallimu* is Form III from *k-l-m*, speech — the same root as *kalīm*, one spoken to, applied to Moses at verse 9 of this sūrah in the tradition's usage, and *kalām*, speech, and *taklīm*, wounding.

The source commentary records a textual variant in interpretation: some report that *speak to them* (tukallimuhum) can be understood to mean "wound them" (taklimuhum) — al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī. The two readings differ by one letter's pointing, and both are recorded. The majority reading is *speaks*, and the wound reading is secondary; but the recording of both by the classical commentators is a lesson in the tradition's handling of ambiguity.

The speech is the verse's central feature, because speech distinguishes this beast from every other *dābbah*. Animals do not speak; a speaking animal is a sign, and the sign's content is given in the final clause.

Al-Naml's own precedents for animal speech are the ant at verse 18 and the hoopoe at verses 22–28 — and the sūrah takes its name from the ant. The commentators note the connection: the sūrah in which an ant speaks and a bird delivers a reasoned report is the sūrah that speaks of a beast that addresses mankind. The pattern is established and the third instance is the eschatological one.

**Anna'l-Nāsa Kānū Bi-Āyātinā Lā Yūqinūn: The Content of the Speech**

*"Because mankind was not certain of Our signs"* — or, as the source commentary notes, most commentators believe the statement *mankind was not certain of Our signs* will be spoken or conveyed by the beast (al-Zamakhsharī).

Two readings of *anna* are possible and both are attested:

**Causal**: "it speaks to them *because* mankind was not certain of Our signs" — the speech is occasioned by the failure of certainty.

**Declarative**: "it speaks to them *that* mankind was not certain of Our signs" — the speech's content is the statement itself.

Al-Zamakhsharī prefers the second: the beast says to people, "mankind was not certain of Our signs." The verse thus presents an eschatological reproach delivered by a creature of the earth, and the reproach is not about the denial of the signs but about the absence of *yaqīn*.

**Lā Yūqinūn: The Failure of Certainty**

*Yūqinūn* is Form IV from *y-q-n*, certainty — the same root as *yaqīn*, certainty, and *mūqinūn*, those who are certain. Verse 3 used the word of the believers: *wa hum bi'l-ākhirati hum yūqinūn* — "and of the Hereafter they are certain." Verse 81 used it of the audience: *man yuʾminu bi-āyātinā* — those who believe in Our signs.

Verse 82 states the failure: mankind *was not certain* of the signs. The negation is not of knowledge but of certainty, and the distinction is essential to the verse.

The people addressed by the signs had information. They had the Qur'an, the messengers, the narratives, the natural signs of verses 86–88, and the arguments of verses 59–64. What they lacked was *yaqīn* — the settled, unshakeable conviction that produces action. Verse 66 described the substitute: *their knowledge stopped short* (*iddāraka ʿilmuhum fī'l-ākhirati*), they were in doubt, they were blind.

Verse 82's reproach therefore names the precise deficiency: not ignorance but insufficient certainty. The tradition developed the concept of *yaqīn* extensively, and Ibn al-Qayyim in *Madārij al-Sālikīn* ranks it in three degrees: *ʿilm al-yaqīn* (the certainty of knowledge — knowing fire burns from testimony), *ʿayn al-yaqīn* (the certainty of sight — seeing the fire), and *ḥaqq al-yaqīn* (the certainty of experience — being touched by the fire). The Qur'an uses all three:

> *"Nay, if you knew with the knowledge of certainty (ʿilma'l-yaqīn), you would surely see the Blaze"* (Qur'an 102:5–6)

> *"Then you shall surely see it with the eye of certainty (ʿayna'l-yaqīn)"* (Qur'an 102:7)

> *"And verily this is the truth of certainty (ḥaqqu'l-yaqīn)"* (Qur'an 56:95; 69:51)

Verse 82's *lā yūqinūn* is the absence of all three degrees in this world, and the beast's speech announces that the deficiency is now visible — the certainty has arrived too late, at the moment when it can no longer benefit.

**The Traditions on the Beast's Description**

The source commentary records the exegetical literature in detail, and it should be presented with the scholarly caution the tradition itself applied.

**Its size**: for some, the beast is enormous, sixty cubits long (al-Rāzī) or seventy cubits (Ibn Kathīr, al-Thaʿlabī).

**Its equipment**: others say it will emerge with the staff of Moses and the seal of Solomon, touch the face of the believers with the staff, and stamp the face of the disbelievers with the seal, so that anyone seeing a person will be able to say, "This is a believer, or this is a disbeliever" (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī).

**Its composite form**: other accounts describe the beast as made up of parts of different animals — the head of a bull, the eyes of a pig, the trunk of an elephant, the horns of a stag, the torso of a lion, the markings of a tiger, the haunches of a cat, the tail of a ram, and the legs of a camel; twelve cubits long. When it touches the believer's face, a white spot appears and grows to cover the face; when it touches the disbeliever's face with the seal, a black spot does the same. It will say: "O so-and-so, you are one of the people of the Garden; and so-and-so, you are one of the people of the Fire" (al-Thaʿlabī).

**Other forms**: some give it the face of a man and the body of a bird; others say its feet will be on the ground but its head in the clouds (Ibn Kathīr).

**Its emergence**: for some it comes forth from Makkah, specifically from Minā; or it arises three times — from Yemen, from the desert, and then from Makkah.

**The Sufi readings**: according to some Sufis, the composite form symbolizes a "universal nature" bearing all worldly realities within it; it is a manifestation of the intermediate world (*barzakh*) between bodies and spirits where opposites coexist, and it will unlock mysteries for those with depth of spiritual vision (Ibn ʿArabī, per the source commentary's IḤ). At another level, some say the beast is an ignorant spiritual master whom one encounters when spiritually veiled, and through whom God punishes those He chooses by miring them in the ignorance of following a fool (al-Jīlī, per Aj).

**The Tradition's Own Caution**

The most important sentence in the source commentary's discussion of verse 82 is al-Rāzī's methodological statement: *since there is no clear indication of any of these details in the Qur'an, one should accept a report only insofar as it can be authenticated as from the Prophet ﷺ, but otherwise one should pay no attention to such details.*

The statement is the classical discipline for eschatological detail, and it should govern the reading. The Qur'an says: a Word falls; a beast is brought forth from the earth; it speaks to them; the content concerns mankind's failure of certainty. Everything beyond that is report, and reports are graded.

Much of the descriptive material is traced to the *Isrāʾīliyyāt* — the traditions transmitted from Jewish and Christian sources — and Ibn Kathīr, while recording them, repeatedly notes their origin and their status. The reliably authenticated material is limited: the beast is one of the major signs; it emerges near the Hour; belief after its emergence does not benefit the one who did not believe before (Muslim 158).

The discipline matters for a general reason. Eschatology is the region of the Qur'an where speculation is most tempting and least disciplined, and verse 82 is the Qur'an's own test case: a verse that states a fact and refuses detail, surrounded by centuries of detail. The reader who follows al-Rāzī's rule will affirm what the verse affirms and decline what it declines.

**The Beast and the Sun's Rising From the West**

The two signs are linked in Muslim 158 and in the wider tradition, and the linkage has an interpretive consequence. Both are signs whose function is not to produce faith but to close the period in which faith is possible.

Verse 6:158 established the principle for the sun's rising from the west, and the same principle governs the beast. The Qur'an's eschatology thus contains a category of sign distinct from the ordinary sign (*āyah*): the ordinary sign invites belief and benefits the believer; the closing sign announces that the period of invitation has ended.

Verse 4:18 states the corresponding principle for the individual:

> *"And repentance is not for those who do evil deeds, until when death comes to one of them he says: 'Now I repent'; nor for those who die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 4:18)

The individual's death is the personal closing; the beast and the sun's rising are the collective one. Verse 82 is the collective statement, and its placement immediately after verses 80–81's declaration that the Prophet ﷺ cannot make the dead hear is structural: those who could not be made to hear in life will encounter a sign that speaks to them when hearing no longer helps.

**The Contextual Reading: When Nobody Enjoins Good**

The source commentary records a detail that gives the verse its moral dimension: *it is said the beast will come upon people at a time when no one any longer "enjoins what is right and forbids what is wrong"* (see 3:104; al-Ṭabarī).

The condition of the beast's emergence is therefore not astronomical but moral: a society in which the command of good and the prohibition of wrong have ceased. The Qur'an makes that command the defining feature of the Muslim community:

> *"You are the best community brought forth for mankind, enjoining what is right, forbidding what is wrong, and believing in God."* (Qur'an 3:110)

> *"And let there be among you a community calling unto good, enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong; and those — they are the successful."* (Qur'an 3:104)

> *"The believers, men and women, are protectors of one another; they enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong"* (Qur'an 9:71)

The ḥadīth literature states the consequence of abandoning it:

> *"By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, you shall enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong, or God will soon send upon you a punishment from Him, and then you will supplicate and it will not be answered."* (al-Tirmidhī 2169; Abū Dāwūd 4338, graded ḥasan)

Verse 82's beast is read in this tradition as the *punishment from Him* — the sign that arrives when the community has ceased to function as a moral community. The reading gives the verse a present-tense application: the condition of the beast's emergence is a condition human beings can observe, and its observation is a warning before the sign.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 82 has three applications, and the third is the one that does not depend on eschatological timing.

**As a statement of the future**: the verse affirms an event that will occur near the Hour, described by the Prophet ﷺ among the major signs. The Muslim affirms it as part of the belief in the Unseen that verse 2 requires (*alladhīna yuʾminūna bi'l-ghayb*), and he declines to specify what the Qur'an declined to specify.

**As a warning about certainty**: the reproach the beast delivers is not "you did not know" but "you were not certain." The distinction addresses the modern condition precisely. The contemporary reader has more information about the Qur'an, its history, its claims, and its critics than any previous generation, and he is not thereby more certain. Verse 66 named the mechanism — knowledge that stops short, doubt, blindness — and verse 82 names the outcome. Information is not certainty, and certainty is not produced by accumulation. It is produced by the heart's settling on what the evidence supports, which is verse 81's *yuʾminu bi-āyātinā*.

**As a diagnosis of the community**: the beast emerges when nobody enjoins good and forbids wrong. The reader can observe whether that condition is approaching in his own community — whether the wrong is being named, whether the good is being urged, whether the naming and urging are being done by anyone, and whether they are being done by him. Verse 82's eschatology is, on this reading, a mirror: the sign is future, and the condition of the sign is present.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:83

> **And on that Day We shall gather from every community a group of those who denied Our signs, and they shall be marshaled [in ordered ranks].**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gathering of the Deniers**

*Wa yawma naḥshuru min kulli ummatin fawjan mimman yukadhdhibu bi-āyātinā fa-hum yūzaʿūn* — "And on the Day We shall gather from every community a group of those who deny Our signs, and they shall be restrained [in ordered ranks]."

Verse 82 described the sign before the Hour. Verse 83 describes the Hour itself, and it begins with the gathering. The verse is the first of a continuous eschatological narrative running from verse 83 to verse 90, and it is the Qur'an's most detailed account of the questioning of the deniers in any Makkah's sūrah.

**Yawma Naḥshuru: The Day We Gather**

*"And on the Day We gather."* *Ḥashara* is from *ḥ-sh-r*, to gather, to assemble, to muster — the verb of the military muster and of the eschatological gathering. Its cognates include *ḥashr*, the gathering, and *al-maḥshar*, the place of gathering.

The Qur'an uses the verb of the Resurrection constantly:

> *"On the Day when We shall gather the righteous unto the Merciful as a delegation"* (Qur'an 19:85)

> *"And We shall drive the criminals unto Hell as a thirsty herd"* (Qur'an 19:86)

> *"On the Day when the trumpet is blown, and We shall gather the criminals on that Day, blue-eyed"* (Qur'an 20:102)

> *"And they ask thee concerning the mountains. Say: 'My Lord will scatter them as dust; and He will leave them a level plain wherein thou seest no crookedness nor curve. On that Day they will follow the Caller — no crookedness in him — and the voices will be humbled before the Merciful, and thou shalt hear nothing but a whisper.'"* (Qur'an 20:105–108)

> *"On the Day when He gathers you for the Day of Gathering — that is the Day of Mutual Loss and Gain"* (Qur'an 64:9)

Verse 19:85–86 gives the two gatherings in contrast: the righteous gathered as a delegation (*wafd*) — the honored reception of guests — and the criminals driven as a thirsty herd (*wird*) — cattle driven to water. Verse 83's gathering is of the second kind, and the specification *minman yukadhdhibu bi-āyātinā* — those who deny Our signs — identifies them.

**Min Kulli Ummatin Fawjan: From Every Community, a Group**

*"From every community a group."* Two terms require attention.

*Ummah* — from *ʾ-m-m*, community, nation, religious community, also a period of time and an imam or leader. The Qur'an uses it of the communities addressed by successive messengers:

> *"And for every community (ummah) there is a messenger; and when their messenger comes, it will be judged between them with justice, and they will not be wronged."* (Qur'an 10:47)

> *"And verily We sent messengers before thee; among them are those We have narrated unto thee, and among them are those We have not narrated unto thee."* (Qur'an 40:78)

> *"Mankind was one community; then God sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warners"* (Qur'an 2:213)

*Fawj* — from *f-w-j*, a group, a company, a delegation traveling together; the plural *afwāj* appears in 78:18:

> *"The Day when the trumpet is blown and you come in groups (afwājan)"* (Qur'an 78:18)

> *"Until when they come to it, its gates are opened, and its keepers say..."* — and: *"And they will be driven unto Hell in groups (zumaran)"* (Qur'an 39:71, 73)

The commentators discuss whether *min* in *min kulli ummatin fawjan* is partitive or explicative. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr hold the partitive reading: from every community, *a group* — that is, not the whole community but a portion of it, specifically those who denied. Al-Qurṭubī notes the reading that the *fawj* is the leading group, the chiefs, gathered first as a sample of the community.

The partitive reading has a specific implication that the commentators draw: the gathering described in verse 83 is not the general Resurrection of all humanity but a preliminary gathering of the deniers from each community, brought forward for questioning before the general assembly. Verse 84's *ḥattā idhā jāʾū* — "until, when they come" — supports the reading, since it describes their arrival at a place of questioning.

The tradition's discussion of the two gatherings — the specific one of verse 83 and the general one of verses like 19:85 — is one of the technical questions of Qur'anic eschatology, and the majority position holds that verse 83 describes a distinct muster of the chiefs and deniers of each community, gathered first and questioned first.

**Mimman Yukadhdhibu Bi-Āyātinā: Those Who Deny Our Signs**

*"Those who deny Our signs."* *Yukadhdhibu* is Form II from *k-dh-b*, to deny, to falsify, to call a liar — the intensive form denoting repeated or emphatic denial. The same root produced *kadhdhabat* at verse 48 (of the Thamūd) and *mukadhdhib* elsewhere; and *āyāt*, signs, is the sūrah's central term — used at verses 1, 12, 14, 26, 46, 47, 59, 81, 82, 83, 84, 86, 90, 92.

The phrase *yukadhdhibu bi-āyātinā* appears repeatedly:

> *"Those who deny Our signs (alladhīna yukadhdhibūna bi-āyātinā) and are arrogant concerning them — the gates of heaven will not be opened for them"* (Qur'an 7:40)

> *"And those who denied Our signs (wa'lladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā) — the punishment shall touch them for what they used to transgress"* (Qur'an 6:49)

> *"Verily those who deny Our signs and are arrogant concerning them"* (Qur'an 4:172)

> *"And those who denied Our signs — they are the companions of the Left"* (Qur'an 90:19)

Verse 7:40 adds the qualification *and are arrogant concerning them* — *wa-stakbarū ʿanhā* — and arrogance is the Qur'an's consistent diagnosis of the denial. Verse 45 of this sūrah used the same word of the Thamūd's faction (*istakbarū*), and verse 34 of the kings (*istaʿlāw*). The denial is not an intellectual conclusion but a posture.

Verse 81 had defined the receptive audience as *those who believe in Our signs* (*yuʾminu bi-āyātinā*); verse 83 defines the gathered group as *those who deny Our signs* (*yukadhdhibu bi-āyātinā*). The two phrases are the sūrah's paired descriptions of the two human responses to the sign, and verses 83–90 describe the fate of the second.

**Fa-Hum Yūzaʿūn: Marshaled, Restrained**

*"And they shall be marshaled."* *Yūzaʿūn* is Form VII passive from *w-z-ʿ*, to restrain, to hold back, to check, to keep in order. The verb's root sense is to prevent someone from doing what he would do — to hold him in.

The Qur'an uses the same verb in an important passage:

> *"And on the Day when those who disbelieve are marshaled (yuḥsharu) unto the Fire — and they will be restrained (fa-hum yūzaʿūn)"* (Qur'an 41:19)

> *"And on the Day when the enemies of God are gathered unto the Fire, they are driven (fahum yūzaʿūn)"* (Qur'an 41:19)

Verse 41:19 is the direct parallel, and the commentators — including al-Ṭabarī, cited in the source commentary — note that *marshaled* here has the same sense as at verse 17 of this sūrah, where Solomon's hosts were described: *wa-ḥushira li-Sulaymāna junūduhu... fahum yūzaʿūn* — "and there were gathered unto Solomon his hosts... and they were marshaled."

The connection between verse 17 and verse 83 is one of al-Naml's most striking structural links, and the source commentary notes it explicitly: *as when used in connection with Solomon's hosts in v. 17, marshaled can mean to corral or group together.*

The link deserves development. In verse 17, the same phrase describes Solomon's army — jinn, men, and birds — gathered and held in ordered ranks, restrained from disorder by the marshals. In verse 83, the same phrase describes the deniers of every community gathered and held in ranks. Solomon's marshals were his officers; the marshals of verse 83 are the angels.

The Qur'an's use of Solomon's kingdom as the image of the eschatological order is consistent with the sūrah's larger method. Solomon's court (vv. 17–44) was a scene of ordered assembly, disciplined ranks, delegated authority, precise reporting, and judgment executed. Verse 83 presents the same features on an eschatological scale, and the parallel is not incidental: the sūrah has prepared the reader for the assembly by narrating one.

**The Discipline of the Gathering**

The word *yūzaʿūn* carries a specific implication about the character of the assembly. The gathered are *restrained* — held, checked, kept in place. They cannot disperse, cannot hide, cannot leave the ranks, cannot advance out of turn.

Verse 78:18 gives the same image from another angle: *wa jīʾa yawmaʾidhin bi-jahannama yawmaʾidhin yatadhakkaru'l-insānu wa annā lahu'l-dhikrā* — "and Hell is brought on that Day; on that Day man remembers, and what avails him the remembering?" The bringing of Hell into view while the ranks stand restrained is the scene verse 83 begins to describe.

Verse 18:48 supplies the restraint's completion:

> *"And they will be presented before thy Lord in ranks: 'Verily you have come unto Us as We created you the first time; but you claimed that We would not appoint for you a tryst.'"* (Qur'an 18:48)

*Ṣaffan* — in ranks — and the taunt *you claimed We would not appoint a tryst* answers the demand of verse 71 of this sūrah: *when will this promise, if you are truthful?* The tryst was appointed; the ranks are formed; the claim has been falsified.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 83 asks the reader to imagine himself in a rank he did not choose to join, at an assembly he cannot leave, awaiting a question he cannot evade.

The imagination is not idle. The verse's force lies in the contrast between the assembly's order and the assembled person's disorder. He lived in a state of self-direction — deciding what to accept, what to deny, what to postpone — and he arrives in a state of complete restraint, held in a rank, waiting for a decision made about him.

The practical instruction the verse implies is the one the tradition drew from the parallel at verse 17: order your assembly before the assembly is ordered for you. The person who arranges his conduct, his obligations, his relations, and his accounts while he has the capacity to arrange them arrives at verse 83 in a different state from the one who left everything disordered. The ranks are the same; the condition of the person standing in them is not.

Verse 34:13's command to the house of David — *work, in gratitude* — and verse 81's definition of the audience — *those who believe in Our signs and submit* — supply the arrangement the verse requires. Belief in the signs and submission are the two acts that determine which group a person is gathered with, and both are available before the gathering.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:84

> **Till, when they come, He will say, "Did you deny My signs, though you encompassed them not in knowledge? What is it that you used to do?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question at the Assembly**

*Ḥattā idhā jāʾū qāla a-kadhdhabtum bi-āyātī wa lam tuḥīṭū bihā ʿilman ammādhā kuntum taʿmalūn* — "Until, when they come, He will say: 'Did you deny My signs while you did not encompass them with knowledge? Or what is it that you used to do?'"

Verse 83 gathered the deniers in ranks. Verse 84 records the question addressed to them, and the question is the sūrah's most searching interrogation. It has two parts, and both are accusations disguised as inquiries.

**Ḥattā Idhā Jāʾū Qāla: The Structure of the Scene**

*"Until, when they come, He said."* The construction *ḥattā idhā* — "until, when" — marks the arrival of a culminating moment after a process, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly in eschatological and narrative scenes:

> *"Until, when they come to it, its gates are opened"* (Qur'an 39:71, 73)

> *"Until, when they reached the setting of the sun, he found it setting in a spring of dark mud"* (Qur'an 18:86)

> *"Until, when the earth was adorned and beautified, and its people thought they were masters of it, Our command came upon it by night or by day"* (Qur'an 10:24)

The speaker is God, indicated by the bare *qāla* — "He said" — and the saying is direct, without intermediary. The scene is the assembly of verse 83, the ranks of verse 17's *yūzaʿūn*, and the question is asked by the One whom verse 78 named as Judge.

**A-Kadhdhabtum Bi-Āyātī: "Did You Deny My Signs?"**

The interrogative *hamzah* — *a-kadhdhabtum* — introduces a question whose answer is known, and in Arabic rhetoric such a question functions as a rebuke and a declaration rather than an inquiry. The form is the same as verse 91's *a-fa-lā tattaqūn* and verse 61's *a-ilāhun maʿa Llāh*, and its function is identical: to establish a fact by asking it.

The verb is Form II *kadhdhabtum* — you denied, you falsified, you called a liar — the second-person plural of the same verb used in the third person at verse 83 (*yukadhdhibu*). The shift from "those who deny" to "did *you* deny" is the moment the accusation becomes personal and addressed.

*"My signs"* — *bi-āyātī* — with the first-person possessive. The signs are God's own, and the denial is therefore of something belonging to the Questioner. The Qur'an's insistence on this possessive is consistent: the signs are not neutral evidence but divine property, and their denial is an offense against the Owner.

Verse 83 used *bi-āyātinā* — "Our signs" — in the majestic plural; verse 84 uses *bi-āyātī* — "My signs" — in the singular. The shift from the plural of majesty to the first-person singular occurs at the moment of direct address, and the effect is intimacy and severity together: not a delegation speaking on behalf of a king, but the Owner speaking of His own property.

**Wa Lam Tuḥīṭū Bihā ʿIlman: "While You Did Not Encompass Them With Knowledge"**

The clause is the verse's most important and most difficult, and the source commentary gives the principal interpretation: *encompassed them not in knowledge* is interpreted to mean that the disbelievers did not know or recognize the signs as they deserved to be known (al-Ṭabarī).

*Tuḥīṭū* is from *ḥ-w-ṭ*, to surround, to enclose, to encompass — the same root as *ḥāʾiṭ*, a wall, and *muḥīṭ*, surrounding, used of God as *muḥīṭ* at 4:126 and 8:47, and of the fire's encompassing at 29:54. The verb denotes complete enclosure on every side.

The Qur'an's use of the verb with knowledge is a technical formula:

> *"And they encompassed it not in knowledge (wa lam yuḥīṭū bihī ʿilman)"* (Qur'an 27:84)

> *"And they killed him not, nor crucified him, but it was made to appear so unto them... And they have thereof no knowledge (wa malahu bihī min ʿilmin); they follow only conjecture"* (Qur'an 4:157)

> *"But they encompassed it not in knowledge"* — cf. 6:131; 10:39; 17:36

> *"And they deny not Our signs save the wrongdoers"* — and: *"Nay, but they denied that whereof they encompassed not the knowledge (bi-mā lam yuḥīṭū bi-ʿilmihī)"* (Qur'an 10:39)

Verse 10:39 is the closest parallel and is decisive for interpretation:

> *"Nay, but they denied that whereof they encompassed not the knowledge (bal kadhdhabū bi-mā lam yuḥīṭū bi-ʿilmihī), and its interpretation had not come unto them. Even so did those before them deny; so behold how was the end of the wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 10:39)

The verse states the mechanism explicitly: they denied what they had not encompassed with knowledge — they rejected it before understanding it — and *its interpretation had not come unto them.* The denial preceded the comprehension.

Verse 84's question is therefore an accusation of *premature denial*: you denied My signs while you did not comprehend them. The denial was not the conclusion of investigation; it was the avoidance of it.

**The Three Readings of the Clause**

The commentators developed the clause in three directions, and all three are recorded.

**You denied without knowing what you denied.** This is al-Ṭabarī's reading, cited in the source commentary: they did not know or recognize the signs as they deserved to be known. The denial was of something not understood, and the responsibility for the non-understanding is theirs, since they did not investigate.

**You denied without the knowledge that would justify denial.** Denial of a claim requires grounds; the deniers had none. Verse 64's demand — *bring your proof, if you are truthful* — is the worldly form of the same requirement, and verse 84 states that at the assembly the requirement will be enforced.

**You denied before the interpretation arrived.** Verse 10:39's *wa lamma yaʾtīhim taʾwīluhu* — "its interpretation had not come unto them" — describes a person who rejects a text before its meaning has been explained to him. The verse's application is to those who dismissed the Qur'an's promises of resurrection before understanding what the Qur'an meant by them.

The three readings converge on one accusation: **the denial was not an act of knowledge but an act of avoidance.** Verse 66 had already established the diagnosis — *their knowledge stopped short, they were in doubt, they were blind* — and verse 84 puts the diagnosis into the Questioner's mouth.

**Ammādhā Kuntum Taʿmalūn: "What Is It That You Used to Do?"**

The second question is shorter and more devastating. *Ammā* — "or what" — combines the interrogative *hamzah* with *mā*, and the construction can be read as a separate question ("and what were you doing?") or as a disjunctive ("or what were you doing?").

*Kuntum taʿmalūn* — "you used to do" — is the imperfect in a past construction denoting habitual or continuous action: what were you *in the habit of doing*, what was your ongoing practice?

The question is a demand for an account of the deeds, and its force lies in what it does not say. It does not ask what they believed, what they thought, what they intended, or what they concluded. It asks what they *did*. Verse 74 established that the interiors are known; verse 75 established that everything is recorded; verse 84 asks the assembled to state their own deeds.

The Qur'an uses the same formula repeatedly:

> *"And We shall question those unto whom messengers were sent, and We shall question the messengers"* (Qur'an 7:6)

> *"On the Day when God gathers them all together, then He says to the angels: 'Is it you these used to worship?' They say: 'Glory be to Thee! Thou art our Protector, not them. Nay, but they worshipped the jinn; most of them were believers in them.'"* — and: *"So today you have no power over one another"* (Qur'an 34:40–42)

> *"And every soul shall come, with it a driver and a witness"* (Qur'an 50:21)

> *"And the Book shall be placed, and thou shalt see the criminals fearful of what is in it, and they shall say: 'Woe unto us! What a Book is this! It omits not a small thing nor a great, but has numbered it.' And they shall find what they did present [before them]"* (Qur'an 18:49)

Verse 18:49 supplies the answer to verse 84's question before it is asked: they will find what they did, present, numbered, omitted in nothing small or great. The question is therefore not an inquiry for information but an opportunity to state one's own case — an opportunity that verse 85 will say cannot be used.

**The Question's Rhetoric: Why Ask What Is Known?**

The commentators address an obvious difficulty: God knows the answer (v. 74), the record exists (v. 75), so why ask? The answers given in the tradition are several and complementary.

**To establish the case against the accused by his own mouth.** A confession is different in kind from evidence produced against a person. The question invites the accused to state his own deeds, and his statement — or his silence — becomes part of the record. Verse 65's *shahīd* (witness) and verse 75's Book supply the other evidence; the question supplies the self-testimony.

**To remove the excuse of ignorance.** The accused cannot claim he was never asked. Verse 17:15 establishes the principle: *And We never punish until We have sent a messenger* — and verse 84's questioning is the messenger's function continued into the assembly.

**To make the accused confront himself.** The question's effect on the hearer is not informational but existential. Being asked "what were you doing?" in the presence of the One who knows is a different experience from having one's deeds read out, and verse 17:14 makes the confrontation the mode of the accounting: *"Read thy Book! Thy own soul suffices as a reckoner against thee this Day."*

**To display the justice of the judgment to the assembly.** The questioning is public — the ranks of verse 83 are present — and the display establishes for every witness that the judgment proceeded from a full hearing. Verse 64's *and they will not be wronged* and verse 21:47's balances are the same concern: the justice must be visible as well as real.

**The Verse's Application: The Two Questions Put to Every Reader**

Verse 84's two questions are addressed to the deniers of the assembly, and the tradition reads them as questions every person will face in some form. They can be put to the reader now, and putting them is the verse's practical use.

**Did you deny what you did not understand?** The question addresses the modern posture toward religion, which is frequently a denial that has not been preceded by comprehension. A person rejects a claim — about revelation, about the afterlife, about prophecy, about divine law — and asked what he understood of the claim before rejecting it, he cannot say. Verse 10:39 named the mechanism: they denied what they had not encompassed with knowledge, and its interpretation had not come to them.

The honest answer to the question requires an inventory: what have I denied, and what did I know of it when I denied it? The inventory is uncomfortable precisely because the answer is usually "very little," and verse 84 states that the answer will be required.

The discipline the question implies is the one verse 64 demanded and verse 36:36 prescribed: do not follow what you have no knowledge of. The denial requires as much investigation as the affirmation, and verse 84 treats uninformed denial as culpable rather than excusable.

**What were you doing?** The second question is simpler and harder. It asks about practice, not belief; about the habitual, not the exceptional; about the ongoing *kuntum taʿmalūn*, not the single act.

The question's form — the past habitual — means the answer is a pattern rather than a list. What was the pattern of your days? What did you do with your time, your money, your speech, your body, your relations, your position? The pattern is what the question seeks, and the pattern is what the record of verse 75 contains.

The Prophet ﷺ's instruction on the question's worldly use is the report at al-Tirmidhī 2417, graded ṣaḥīḥ:

> *"The feet of the son of Adam will not move on the Day of Resurrection until he is asked about four things: his life — in what he spent it; his youth — in what he wore it out; his wealth — from where he acquired it and in what he spent it; and his knowledge — what he did with it."* (al-Tirmidhī 2417)

The four questions are verse 84's *ammādhā kuntum taʿmalūn* itemized: life, youth, wealth, knowledge. The report adds the detail that the feet will not move until the questions are answered — the restraint of verse 83's *yūzaʿūn* — and it supplies the practical preparation the verse requires: answer the questions now, while the answering can change the answer.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:85

> **And the Word will come upon them for their having done wrong, and they shall not speak.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence and the Silence**

*Wa waqaʿa'l-qawlu ʿalayhim bi-mā ẓalamū fa-hum lā yanṭiqūn* — "And the Word falls upon them for what they wronged, and they do not speak."

Verse 84 asked the questions. Verse 85 records the sentence and the silence that follows it, and the verse is one of the Qur'an's most compressed eschatological statements: nine words containing the verdict, its ground, and its effect.

**Wa Waqaʿa'l-Qawl: The Word Falls**

*"And the Word falls upon them."* The phrase is identical in form to verse 82's *waqaʿa'l-qawl* — *waqaʿa*, fell, occurred, came due; *al-qawl*, the Word. The verb's root sense is of a blow landing or a bird alighting, and its use for the divine Word gives the Word the character of an event that arrives and cannot be deflected.

Verse 82 described the Word falling as the condition for the beast's emergence. Verse 85 describes the Word falling as the sentence upon the assembled. The two uses of the identical phrase at three verses' distance are deliberate, and they connect the eschatological sign (82) with the eschatological judgment (85) by a single term.

The source commentary notes the parallel language about the Word "coming due": 10:96, 28:63, 36:7, 70, 39:71, 40:6 — connoting the coming of Divine Judgment, and al-Ṭabarī refers to 10:33 and 36:7.

> *"And the Word came due upon them (wa waqaʿa'l-qawlu ʿalayhim) for what they wronged, so they do not speak."* (Qur'an 27:85)

> *"And the Word came due upon most of them, so they do not believe."* (Qur'an 36:7)

> *"Thus has the Word of thy Lord come due upon those who do wrong."* (Qur'an 10:33)

> *"And they will say: 'Our Lord! Thou madest us to die twice, and Thou madest us to live twice; and we acknowledge our sins. Is there a way of going out?'"* (Qur'an 40:11)

**ʿAlayhim: Upon Them**

The preposition *ʿalā* — upon — indicates that the Word lands *on* them, not merely concerning them. The image is of a weight or a blow descending onto the subject, and the Qur'an uses the same construction for punishment:

> *"And the Word came due upon them"* (Qur'an 39:71)

> *"And the Word fell upon them"* — and the punishment's arrival is described as *nuzūl* (descending) and *wuqūʿ* (falling) throughout

Verse 65 of this sūrah used *ʿalā* of God's knowledge — *"none in the heavens and the earth knows the Unseen save God"* — and verse 82 used it of the Word falling *upon* them. The preposition is the Qur'an's standard for a divine act reaching its object.

**Bi-Mā Ẓalamū: "For What They Wronged"**

*"For their having done wrong."* The construction *bi-mā* — "because of what" — with the verb supplies the ground of the sentence, and the ground is *ẓulm*.

*Ẓulm* is from *ẓ-l-m*, wrong, injustice, oppression — the root analyzed at verses 11 (of Moses' self-wronging: *ẓalamtu nafsī*) and 14 (of God's not wronging: *wa mā ana ẓallāmun li'l-ʿabīd* — cf. 50:29). The word's root sense is to place a thing in other than its proper place, and its theological range covers injustice to others, injustice to oneself, and the wrong of associating partners with God.

The Qur'an identifies the specific wrong at verse 85 with precision, and the source commentary records it: *the wrong that the disbelievers have done was their denial of the truth and rejection of God's Revelation* (al-Ṭabarī).

> *"Those who believe and confound not their belief with wrong (ẓulm) — theirs is security, and they are guided"* (Qur'an 6:82)

> *"Verily association [with God] is a great wrong (la-ẓulmun ʿaẓīm)"* (Qur'an 31:13)

> *"And who does greater wrong than he who is reminded of the signs of his Lord, then turns away from them, and forgets what his hands sent forward?"* (Qur'an 18:57; 32:22; 6:157)

Verse 18:57 is the precise identification: the greatest wrong is being reminded of the signs and turning away — which is verse 80's image of the deaf who turn their backs retreating, and verse 84's accusation of denying the signs without encompassing them.

Verse 6:82 supplies the counterpart, and the tradition's reading of it is well known: the companions asked what *ẓulm* meant there, and the Prophet ﷺ answered by citing 31:13 — *verily association is a great wrong* — establishing that the *ẓulm* excluding security and guidance is *shirk*. (al-Bukhārī 32, 3360; Muslim 124)

**The Causal Clause's Significance**

*"For what they wronged"* is the verse's guarantee of justice. The Word does not fall arbitrarily, without ground, or for what they did not do. It falls *bi-mā ẓalamū* — because of the specific wrong they committed.

The Qur'an states the principle repeatedly and emphatically:

> *"And thy Lord wrongs not the servants"* (Qur'an 50:29 — quoted at v. 11 of this sūrah)

> *"Verily God does not wrong [people] an atom's weight"* (Qur'an 4:40)

> *"And thy Lord is not unjust toward the servants"* (Qur'an 41:46)

> *"That is for what thy hands sent forward, and because God is not unjust toward the servants"* (Qur'an 22:10; 3:182; 8:51)

Verse 85's causal clause is the al-Naml instance of the principle, and it answers a question the reader may have raised at verse 84: why is the denial so serious? Because it is *ẓulm* — the misplacement of a thing from its proper place, and the thing misplaced is the sign of God.

**Fa-Hum Lā Yanṭiqūn: "And They Do Not Speak"**

*"And they shall not speak."* *Yanṭiqūn* is from *n-ṭ-q*, to speak, to utter — the verb of articulate speech, distinct from *qāla* (to say) in that *naṭaqa* emphasizes the production of speech by a being capable of it. The Qur'an uses the verb of inanimate things given speech:

> *"And We set a seal upon their mouths, and their hands speak unto Us (tukallimunā aydīhim), and their feet bear witness to what they used to earn"* (Qur'an 36:65)

> *"The Day when their tongues and their hands and their feet bear witness against them as to what they used to do"* (Qur'an 24:24)

Verse 36:65 is the direct parallel and explains verse 85's silence: the mouths are sealed and the limbs speak. The faculty of articulate speech is removed, and the testimony is transferred to the body that performed the deeds.

The source commentary notes the interpretation: *they shall not speak* means they will not be able to defend themselves (al-Ṭabarī). The silence is not the absence of anything to say but the removal of the capacity to say it in defense.

**The Qur'an's Vocabulary of Eschatological Silence**

The Qur'an describes the silence of the Day in several registers, and the tradition distinguished them carefully.

**Not permitted to speak**:

> *"This is a Day when they will not speak, nor will it be permitted them to plead"* (Qur'an 77:35–36)

> *"And [they] will not speak save he whom the Merciful permits, and he will say what is right"* (Qur'an 78:38)

**Not able to speak**:

> *"And the Word fell upon them for what they wronged, so they do not speak"* (Qur'an 27:85)

> *"And they will not speak"* — cf. 28:66

> *"On that Day they will follow the Caller... and the voices will be humbled before the Merciful, and thou shalt hear nothing but a whisper"* (Qur'an 20:108)

**Speaking but not heard or not availing**:

> *"And they will call: 'O Mālik! Let thy Lord make an end of us.' He will say: 'Verily you are to remain'"* (Qur'an 43:77)

> *"Our Lord! Take us out; we will work righteousness, other than what we used to work"* — and: *"Did We not give you a life long enough for one who would remember to remember therein?"* (Qur'an 35:37)

Verse 77:35–36 is the most absolute statement: *they will not speak, nor will it be permitted them to plead* — *wa lā yuʾdhanu lahum fayaʿtadhirūn*. The plea (*iʿtidhār*) — the excuse, the defense — is not permitted.

Verse 78:38 supplies the exception and its condition: speech is permitted only to *him whom the Merciful permits*, and only if *he says what is right* — *wa qāla ṣawābā*. The permission is by mercy and the content must be correct, and neither condition is met by the deniers of verse 83.

Verse 28:66 states the corresponding loss of argument:

> *"On that Day the arguments will be obscure to them (fa-ʿamiyat ʿalayhimu'l-anbāʾ), and they will not question one another."* (Qur'an 28:66)
