The Qur'an uses *ʿamiyat* — from the same root as *ʿamūn* at verse 66 of this sūrah — for the obscuring of the arguments. The blind of verse 81 (*al-ʿumy*) arrive at a Day on which their arguments are blinded.

**The Silence as Punishment**

The source commentary directs attention to a further dimension: *the notion that the wretched will or will not be able to speak in the Hereafter to God is discussed in the commentary on 2:174 — "God will not speak to them on the Day of Resurrection" (cf. 3:77) — which describes how not being able to speak with God is a form of punishment.*

> *"Verily those who conceal what God has sent down of the Book, and sell it for a small price — those consume nothing in their bellies but the Fire; and God will not speak to them on the Day of Resurrection (wa lā yukallimuhumu Llāhu yawma'l-qiyāmah), nor will He purify them, and theirs is a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 2:174)

> *"Verily those who purchase a small price with the covenant of God and their own oaths — those shall have no portion in the Hereafter, and God will not speak to them (wa lā yukallimuhumu Llāh), nor look at them, on the Day of Resurrection, nor purify them, and theirs is a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 3:77)

The two verses describe the withholding of God's speech as a punishment, and the mechanism is the withdrawal of relation: to be spoken to is to be recognized, and the refusal of speech is the refusal of recognition.

Verse 85's *lā yanṭiqūn* is the corresponding withholding from the accused's side, and the two together constitute the total silence of the assembly: God does not address them with recognition, and they cannot address Him with defense. Verse 76:11 supplies the counterpart for the righteous — *He will address them* — and verse 36:58 states the highest form:

> *"'Peace' — a saying from a Merciful Lord"* (Qur'an 36:58)

The greeting of *salām* from God is the speech withheld from the deniers and given to the believers, and verse 27 of this sūrah — the sūrah named for the ant — ends its eschatological section at verse 89 with the greeting of peace.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 85 has an application that does not require the reader to imagine the Day, because the verse describes a silence that has worldly analogues and worldly preparation.

**The silence of the one who has no case.** The person who has denied what he did not investigate (v. 84), who has wronged by turning from the signs (v. 85), arrives at a point where he cannot speak. The worldly analogue is familiar: the person who has acted without principle is silent when principle is required of him; the person who has spoken carelessly has nothing to say when his words are read back; the person who has taken without giving has no argument when the account is presented.

Verse 85 states that the silence will be complete and that it will be the consequence of specific wrongs rather than of general inadequacy. The preparation is therefore specific: address the wrongs. Verse 11 of this sūrah gave the model — Moses' *ẓalamtu nafsī* followed immediately by *faghfir lī* and the forgiveness granted — and verse 46 gave the communal form: *seek forgiveness of your Lord, then repent unto Him.*

**The silence of the one whose limbs will speak.** Verse 36:65's sealing of the mouths and speech of the hands means that the body is a witness against its owner. The person who used his hands for taking what was not his, his feet for going where he should not, his tongue for saying what was false, will find the members testifying while he is silent.

The preparation is the one the tradition states in the ḥadīth at al-Bukhārī 6501 and Muslim 2637:

> *"God will say: 'O son of Adam, I was sick and thou didst not visit Me'..."* — and: *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

The second report is the worldly practice of verse 85: the person who speaks good or remains silent has trained the tongue, and his tongue will not testify against him.

**The silence that is a mercy.** There is a final reading of verse 85's silence that the tradition notes. The silence is also a mercy in one respect: the accused is spared the experience of articulating his own condemnation. Verse 17:14 places the reading in the person's own mouth: *"Read thy Book! Thy own soul suffices as a reckoner against thee."* The soul does the reckoning; the mouth is sealed; and the sealing prevents the person from having to speak his own sentence aloud.

The mercy does not remove the punishment. It shapes it, and the shape is verse 85's: the Word falls, the ground is the wrong, and the response is silence.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:86

> **Have they not considered that We made the night that they might rest therein, and the day by which to see? Truly in that are signs for a people who believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return to the Natural Signs**

*Awa lam yaraw annā jaʿalnā'llayla li-yaskunū fīhi wa'l-nahāra mubṣiran inna fī dhālika la-āyātin li-qawmin yuʾminūn* — "Have they not seen that We made the night that they might rest therein, and the day for seeing? Verily in that are signs for a people who believe."

Verse 85 concluded the interrogation of the deniers. Verse 86 turns abruptly from the eschatological scene to the ordinary alternation of night and day, and the turn is one of al-Naml's characteristic moves: the argument from the Day returns to the argument from the world.

The verse's placement after the assembly has a specific rhetorical function. The reader has just witnessed the silence of those who denied the signs. Verse 86 asks whether he has *seen* the signs — and the signs offered are the two he experiences every day.

**Awa Lam Yaraw: "Have They Not Seen?"**

The construction combines the interrogative *hamzah*, the conjunction *wa*, and the negative *lam* with the jussive — *a-wa lam yaraw* — producing "have they not seen?", and the form is one of the Qur'an's most frequent argumentative openings.

The same construction appeared at verse 69 of this sūrah — *a-wa lam yasīrū fī'l-arḍi fa-yanẓurū* — "have they not traveled in the earth and beheld?" — and the two verses are complementary: verse 69's seeing is historical (travel and look at the ruins), verse 86's seeing is cosmological (look at the night and the day).

The Qur'an uses the formula extensively:

> *"Have they not seen (a-wa lam yaraw) how many a generation We destroyed before them?"* (Qur'an 6:6; 32:26)

> *"Have they not considered (a-wa lam yatadabbarū) within themselves?"* (Qur'an 30:8)

> *"Have they not considered (awalam yanẓurū) in the dominion of the heavens and the earth?"* (Qur'an 7:185)

> *"Have they not seen the birds above them, spreading and folding? None holds them save the Merciful"* (Qur'an 67:19)

Verse 30:8's verb is *yatadabbarū* — from *d-b-r*, to consider, to reflect upon the end of a matter — and verse 86's *yaraw* is from *r-ʾ-y*, to see, to perceive, to consider. The two verbs describe the act verse 66 said was missing: reflection.

**Annā Jaʿalnā'llayla Li-Yaskunū Fīhi: "That We Made the Night That They Might Rest"**

*"That We made the night that they might rest therein."* *Jaʿalnā* is from *j-ʿ-l*, to make, to appoint, to constitute — the verb of making one thing into or for another. *Li-yaskunū* is from *s-k-n*, to be still, to rest, to dwell, to be tranquil — the same root as *sakīnah*, tranquility (which descended on the believers at verse 40 in some readings, and at 48:4, 26), and *maskan*, dwelling.

*Li-yaskunū* carries the lam of purpose — "in order that they might rest" — and the construction states that the night was *made for* rest. The making is intentional and the intention is specified.

The source commentary lists the parallels: cf. 10:67, 17:12, 25:47, 40:61; and it notes that God created the night for rest and repose also at 28:73 and 78:9.

> *"It is He Who made for you the night that you may rest therein, and the day for seeing. Verily in that are signs for a people who hear."* (Qur'an 10:67)

> *"And We made the night a garment (libāsan), and We made the day for livelihood (maʿāshan)."* (Qur'an 78:10–11)

> *"And it is He Who made the night a garment, and sleep a repose (subātan), and He made the day a rising (nushūran)."* (Qur'an 25:47)

> *"And of His mercy He made for you the night and the day, that you may rest therein and that you may seek of His bounty, and haply you may give thanks."* (Qur'an 28:73)

> *"God is He Who made for you the night that you may rest therein, and the day for seeing. Verily God is Possessed of Bounty toward mankind, but most of them do not give thanks."* (Qur'an 40:61)

> *"And We made your sleep a repose (subātan)"* (Qur'an 78:9)

Verse 40:61 is the closest parallel and the most significant, because it ends with the identical statement as verse 73 of this sūrah: *wa lākinna aktharahum lā yashkurūn* — "but most of them do not give thanks." The two sūrahs — al-Naml and Ghāfir — pair the night-and-day sign with the ingratitude statement, and verse 86 of al-Naml stands thirteen verses after its own version of that statement (v. 73).

Verse 28:73 adds the purpose clause's second half and its consequence: *that you may rest therein, and that you may seek of His bounty, and haply you may give thanks* — *wa laʿallakum tashkurūn*. The seeking of bounty is the day's function, the resting is the night's, and gratitude is the intended response to both.

Verse 25:47 gives the three terms in their most compact form: the night as *libās* — a garment, covering; sleep as *subāt* — a repose, a cutting off; the day as *nushūr* — a rising, a resurrection, a scattering forth. The word *nushūr* is significant: it is the same root as the resurrection (*nashr*), and the daily rising is a figure of the eschatological one.

**Wa'l-Nahāra Mubṣiran: "And the Day for Seeing"**

*"And the day for seeing."* *Mubṣir* is the active participle of Form IV *abṣara*, to see, to perceive — and its application to the *day* rather than to the person is a striking grammatical feature that the commentators discuss.

The literal sense is "the seeing day" or "the day that makes see," and al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, and al-Qurṭubī explain it as an attribution of the day's effect to the day itself: the day is described as seeing because seeing occurs in it, as one says "a night of vigil" or "a day of battle." Al-Zamakhsharī reads it as *munīran* — illuminating — since the day's function is to make vision possible by providing light.

The construction pairs the two purposes precisely: the night is *for resting* (li-yaskunū — a purpose clause with the people as subject), and the day is *seeing* (mubṣiran — an adjective with the day as subject). The night's benefit is stated as the people's action; the day's benefit is stated as the day's property. The asymmetry reflects the two functions: rest is something the person does in the night; seeing is something the day makes possible.

Verse 10:67 uses the identical construction — *wa'l-nahāra mubṣiran* — and adds a significant variation in the concluding clause: *inna fī dhālika la-āyātin li-qawmin yasmaʿūn* — "signs for a people who *hear*" — where verse 86 has *li-qawmin yuʾminūn* — "signs for a people who *believe*."

The variation is instructive. Hearing is the faculty that receives the sign; belief is the response that accepts it. Al-Naml's choice of belief follows from verses 80–81, which established that hearing is not achievable by human effort and that the audience consists of those who believe in the signs. The sign is for the believer because the believer is the one for whom the sign functions.

**Inna Fī Dhālika La-Āyātin: "Verily in That Are Signs"**

The clause uses the emphatic *inna* and *lām* and the plural *āyāt* — signs. The plural is significant: the alternation of night and day is not one sign but many, and the commentators enumerate them.

The sign of **the alternation itself** — two states succeeding one another without interruption, for billions of years, without a single failure:

> *"And of His signs is the night and the day, and the sun and the moon"* (Qur'an 41:37)

> *"He makes the night pass into the day and the day pass into the night"* (Qur'an 22:61; 31:29; 35:13; 57:6)

The source commentary lists the night-and-day passages: the night and day passing into one another (3:27, 22:61, 31:29, 35:13, 57:6) and the alternation of the night and the day (2:164, 3:190, 10:6, 13:3, 17:12, 22:61, 24:44, 31:29, 35:13, 39:5, 45:5).

> *"Verily in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the alternation of the night and the day (ikhtilāfi'l-layli wa'l-nahār), are signs for those possessed of intellect (ūlū'l-albāb)"* (Qur'an 3:190)

Verse 3:190's *ūlū'l-albāb* — possessors of intellect — and verse 86's *qawmin yuʾminūn* — a people who believe — are complementary qualifications, and verse 3:191 describes what the *ūlū'l-albāb* do with the sign: they remember God standing, sitting, and on their sides, and they reflect upon the creation of the heavens and the earth.

The sign of **the purposeful arrangement** — the night is *for* rest and the day *for* seeing, which means the arrangement serves the creatures living in it:

> *"Say: 'Have you considered — if God made the night continuous over you until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God would bring you light? Will you not then hear?' Say: 'Have you considered — if God made the day continuous over you until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God would bring you a night in which you rest? Will you not then see?'"* (Qur'an 28:71–72)

Verse 28:71–72 is the counterfactual argument, and it is one of the Qur'an's most complete demonstrations: remove either term of the alternation and human life becomes impossible. The argument proceeds from the *considered* possibility to the impossibility of any other god supplying the missing term, and it ends with the two questions *will you not hear?* and *will you not see?* — the same faculties verse 80 declared incapable in the deaf and verse 81 named in the audience.

The sign of **the measurable order** — the alternation produces time, and time produces the calendar, the prayer times, the months, and the reckoning:

> *"It is He Who made the sun a shining glory and the moon a light, and measured for it stages that you might know the number of years and the reckoning"* (Qur'an 10:5)

> *"They ask thee about the new moons. Say: 'They are appointed times for mankind and for the pilgrimage'"* (Qur'an 2:189)

Verse 10:5's *ḥusāb* — reckoning — connects to verse 71's *mā dhā tastaʿjilūn* and to the objectors' demand for a date: the alternation they experience daily is the instrument by which dates are reckoned, and the One who made the instrument controls the date.

The sign of **the daily resurrection** — sleep as a little death and waking as a little rising:

> *"God takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that die not, in their sleep; and He keeps those upon whom He has decreed death, and sends the others back until an appointed term"* (Qur'an 39:42)

> *"And He it is Who takes you back (yatawaffākum) by night, and knows what you earned by day, then He revives you therein that a term appointed may be fulfilled"* (Qur'an 6:60)

Verse 39:42 is the Qur'an's most explicit statement of the parallel: the soul is taken in sleep as in death, and some are returned while others are kept. The person who sleeps and wakes has undergone a rehearsal of the resurrection, daily, and verse 86's sign contains it.

**Li-Qawmin Yuʾminūn: The Qualification**

*"For a people who believe."* The qualification appears at verse 2 (*hudan wa bushrā li'l-muʾminīn*), verse 77 (*hudan wa raḥmatun li'l-muʾminīn*), verse 81 (*man yuʾminu bi-āyātinā*), and verse 86 (*li-qawmin yuʾminūn*). The repetition is the sūrah's insistence that the signs function for the believer.

The Qur'an varies the qualification across its sign-passages, and the variation is systematic:

> *"Signs for those possessed of intellect (ūlū'l-albāb)"* (Qur'an 3:190)

> *"Signs for a people who reflect (yatafakkarūn)"* (Qur'an 10:24; 13:3; 16:69; 30:21; 45:13)

> *"Signs for a people who understand (yaʿqilūn)"* (Qur'an 2:242; 10:67; 13:4; 30:28)

> *"Signs for a people who hear (yasmaʿūn)"* (Qur'an 10:67)

> *"Signs for the believers (li'l-muʾminīn)"* (Qur'an 15:77; 27:86; 30:46)

> *"Signs for those who have certainty (li'l-mūqinīn)"* (Qur'an 45:20)

> *"Signs for a people who know (yaʿlamūn)"* (Qur'an 30:22, 58)

> *"Signs for a people who fear God (yattaqūn)"* (Qur'an 30:28)

> *"Signs for the worlds (li'l-ʿālamīn)"* (Qur'an 3:96; 15:77)

The list is the Qur'an's taxonomy of receptivity, and verse 86 places belief at the center of it for the night-and-day sign. Verse 82's reproach — *mankind was not certain of Our signs* — used *yaqīn*, and verse 86's qualification uses *īmān*; the two are the sūrah's paired terms for the reception that makes a sign function.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 86 asks the reader to look at the two features of his existence he has never examined: the night and the day.

He has slept in the night and worked in the day every day of his life, and he has almost certainly never considered that the arrangement is *for* him — that the night was made *in order that* he might rest, and the day *in order that* he might see. The verse's claim is that this arrangement is a sign, and that the sign is available to anyone who looks.

The looking requires three acts.

**Notice the fit.** The human body requires sleep; the night provides the darkness, the cooling, and the cessation of activity that make sleep possible. The human body requires activity, vision, work, and food; the day provides the light and the warmth that make them possible. The fit is exact, and it has held for the whole of human history without adjustment. Verse 86 asks whether the fit has been noticed.

**Consider the counterfactual.** Verse 28:71–72 supplies the exercise: imagine continuous night, imagine continuous day. Neither is survivable. The person who performs the exercise arrives at the Qur'an's question — *what god other than God would bring you light?* — and the question has no answer, which is the point.

**Respond with what verse 86 requires.** The sign is *for a people who believe*, and belief here is not mere assent but the response the sign invites: gratitude for the rest and for the sight, and the ordering of one's life around the two. The tradition built an entire discipline on the ordering — the prayer times distributed across the day and night (11:114; 17:78), the night prayer (17:79; 73:1–4), the seeking of bounty in the day (28:73; 62:10) — and the discipline is the practical acknowledgment of verse 86's sign.

The person who sleeps at night and works by day and never connects the pattern to its Maker has the sign and does not have the belief verse 86 names. The person who connects them has performed the act the verse requests, and the act takes no time and requires no learning. It is the recognition, in the morning and at night, that the arrangement is a gift.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:87

> **And on that Day the trumpet will be blown, and whosoever is in the heavens and on the earth will be terrified, save whom God wills, and all will come to Him in abject humility.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Trumpet and the Terror**

*Wa yawma yunfakhu fī'l-ṣūri fa-faziʿa man fī'l-samāwāti wa man fī'l-arḍi illā man shāʾa Llāh; wa kullun atawhu dākhirīn* — "And on the Day the trumpet is blown, those in the heavens and those in the earth are terrified, save whom God wills; and all come to Him, humbled."

Verse 86 offered the sign of night and day. Verse 87 returns to the Day, and it names the event that begins it: the blowing of the trumpet. The verse is al-Naml's account of the *nafkh al-ṣūr* — the Trumpet-blow — and of the universal terror that follows, and it is one of the Qur'an's most complete single-verse descriptions of the moment.

**Yawma Yunfakhu Fī'l-Ṣūr: The Day the Trumpet Is Blown**

*"And on the Day the trumpet is blown."* The source commentary notes that some read this verse as "[Remember] the Day when the trumpet will be blown" (al-Qurṭubī), supplying an implied imperative — *udhkur*, remember — which is standard for Qur'anic *yawm* constructions at the start of an eschatological passage.

*Ṣūr* is from *ṣ-w-r*, and it is generally understood as a horn or trumpet blown by an angel. The source commentary records the interpretive range:

- Although many interpret *trumpet* (*ṣūr*) to be something like a "horn" (*qarn*), it could also be interpreted as an army being mustered by a trumpeter to symbolize the raising of the dead (al-Rāzī).
- Some read *ṣūr* as *ṣuwar*, the plural of *ṣūrah*, "form," meaning that the blowing would be the blowing of spirit into the forms or bodies to resurrect them (al-Rāzī).

The three readings are recorded without the tradition settling definitively on one, and the majority position — held by al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī — is the literal horn, blown by Isrāfīl (Seraphiel).

The Qur'an's trumpet passages, listed by the source commentary: 6:73, 18:99, 20:102, 23:101, 36:51, 39:68, 50:20, 69:13, 74:8, 78:18.

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown (wa nufikha fī'l-ṣūr) — that is the Day of the Threat"* (Qur'an 50:20)

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown, whereupon whosoever is in the heavens and whosoever is in the earth shall swoon, save whom God wills. Then it shall be blown a second time, and behold they stand up, beholding."* (Qur'an 39:68)

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown, and behold, from the graves they will hasten unto their Lord"* (Qur'an 36:51)

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown, and We shall gather the criminals on that Day, blue-eyed"* (Qur'an 20:102)

> *"The Day when the trumpet is blown and you come in groups"* (Qur'an 78:18)

> *"And the earth and the mountains are lifted and crushed with a single crushing"* (Qur'an 69:14)

> *"And when the trumpet is blown with one blowing, and the earth and the mountains are lifted and crushed with a single crushing — that Day the Occurrence occurs"* (Qur'an 69:13–15)

Verse 39:68 is the most complete account and is central to the tradition's discussion of the number of blows, which the source commentary records:

**Three blows**: some mention that this terrifying blowing will be the first of three; the second will cause all to swoon, and the third will bring about the Resurrection (al-Ṭabarī).

**Two blows**: others think the trumpet will only be blown twice, because the one mentioned in this verse is the same as the one causing swooning mentioned in 39:68 (al-Qurṭubī).

The two-blow position is supported by the ḥadīth at al-Bukhārī 4814 and Muslim 2954 from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"Between the two blowings there will be forty." They said: "O Abū Hurayrah, forty days?" He said: "I refuse [to specify]. They said: "Forty months?" He said: "I refuse." They said: "Forty years?" He said: "I refuse. Then God sends down water from the sky, and they grow as vegetables grow. There is nothing of a human being that does not decay, save one bone — the coccyx — and from it creation is reassembled on the Day of Resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 4814; Muslim 2954)

The report's refusal to specify the interval is the tradition's model for eschatological detail: the blowings are affirmed, the interval is not known, and the transmitter declines to guess. That is al-Rāzī's rule at verse 82 applied here.

**The Ḥadīth of the Trumpet**

The tradition preserves an extended report known as *ḥadīth al-ṣūr*, transmitted by ʿAṭiyyah from Ibn ʿAbbās and by others, describing the blows in detail. Ibn Kathīr records it in his *Tafsīr* at verse 6:73 and in *al-Nihāyah*, and he notes that its chain has weakness while its content is supported by other reports. The report describes:

- The first blow: the blow of terror (*nafkhat al-fazaʿ*), at which the inhabitants of the heavens and the earth are terrified — this is verse 87's blow.
- The second blow: the blow of swooning (*nafkhat al-ṣaʿq*), at which all die save whom God wills — 39:68's first blow.
- The third blow: the blow of rising (*nafkhat al-qiyām*), at which all stand before the Lord of the Worlds — 39:68's second blow.

The report explains why verse 87 uses *faziʿa* (were terrified) while 39:68 uses *ṣaʿiqa* (swooned): they are different events, and verse 87 describes the earlier one.

The three-blow reading has the advantage of harmonizing all the Qur'anic passages, since 20:102 describes the criminals gathered blue-eyed at a blow, 36:51 describes them hastening from the graves at a blow, and 39:68 describes swooning and then rising at two blows. Al-Ṭabarī's three-blow account accommodates all four descriptions.

**Fa-Faziʿa: The Verb of Terror**

*"Will be terrified."* *Faziʿa* is from *f-z-ʿ*, terror, fright, panic — the same root as *fazaʿ*, fear, and *faziʿ*, terrified. The Qur'an uses the verb in a second sense that the source commentary records:

*Be terrified* renders the verb *faziʿa*, which can also have the meaning of responding to a summons with alacrity, and hence would describe the way in which those who are resurrected emerge from the graves (al-Qurṭubī).

The dual sense is remarkable: *faziʿa* means both to be terrified and to respond quickly to a call — the reflex of a person startled into immediate action. Al-Qurṭubī's reading takes the second sense: at the blow, the resurrected respond to the summons with the alacrity of the startled, coming out of the graves rapidly.

The two senses are not contradictory in the scene the verse describes. The terror and the alacrity are one response: those who hear the blow are terrified and move immediately, as a person startled by a sudden sound both flinches and turns.

Verse 21:103 supplies the corresponding statement about the terror's absence for the believers:

> *"The Greatest Terror will not grieve them (lā yaḥuzunuhumu'l-fazaʿu'l-akbar), and the angels will meet them: 'This is your Day which you were promised.'"* (Qur'an 21:103)

Verse 21:103's *al-fazaʿ al-akbar* — the Greatest Terror — is the same root as verse 87's *faziʿa*, and the verse promises that the believers will not be grieved by it. Verse 89 will state the same promise in al-Naml's terms: *and that Day they will be secure from terror* — *wa hum min fazaʿin yawmaʾidhin āminūn*.

**Man Fī'l-Samāwāti Wa Man Fī'l-Arḍ: The Universal Scope**

*"Whosoever is in the heavens and on the earth."* The formula is the Qur'an's standard for the totality of created beings, and it appeared at verse 65 of this sūrah in the context of knowledge of the Unseen: *lā yaʿlamu man fī'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi'l-ghayba illa'Llāh*.

The scope includes the angels, the jinn, and human beings — all created beings subject to the blow. Verse 39:68 states the same scope for the swooning, and both verses add the exception.

**Illā Man Shāʾa Llāh: "Save Whom God Wills"**

The exception clause is the verse's theological center, and the source commentary records the interpretive range in detail:

**The martyrs**: *save whom God wills* is thought by some to refer to those slain in the way of God, as mentioned in 3:169, which describes them as alive with their Lord, provided for; they are exempt from the terror that will affect those alive in the world (al-Ṭabarī).

> *"And reckon not those slain in the way of God as dead; nay, they are alive with their Lord, provided for."* (Qur'an 3:169)

**Certain angels**: for others the exception refers to certain angels, or specifically to Gabriel, Michael, Seraphiel (Isrāfīl), and the Angel of Death (ʿIzrāʾīl), and may also include the prophets and saints (al-Jīlī, per Aj).

**Those secured by God**: others are saved from this terror as described in verse 89 (the source commentary) — that is, the exception refers to those whom verse 89 describes as secure from the terror.

The three readings are compatible, and the tradition generally holds that the exception covers several categories: the martyrs alive with their Lord, the great angels, the prophets, and the believers whom God secures from the Greatest Terror (21:103).

The clause's grammatical form — *illā man shāʾa Llāh* — attributes the exception to God's will without specifying its objects, and the unspecified form is the verse's discipline. The reader knows that an exception exists, that it is by God's will, and that the categories are supplied by other texts; he does not know its full membership, and the Qur'an does not tell him.

**Wa Kullun Atawhu Dākhirīn: "And All Come to Him, Humbled"**

*"And all come to Him in abject humility."* *Dākhirīn* is the active plural participle of *dakhara*, to be humbled, abased, humiliated, brought low — a word denoting not merely modesty but the crushing of pretension.

The Qur'an uses the term repeatedly:

> *"And to God prostrates whatever is in the heavens and whatever is in the earth, of creature and angels, and they are not arrogant"* — and: *"And unto God belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth"* — cf. 16:49–50

> *"And when they are thrown into a narrow place thereof, chained together, they will call there for destruction"* — and: *"Call not this Day for one destruction, but call for many destructions"* (Qur'an 25:13–14)

> *"Is it other than the religion of God they seek, while unto Him submits (aslama) whatever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly (ṭawʿan wa karhan), and unto Him they are returned?"* (Qur'an 3:83)

> *"And the voices will be humbled (khashaʿati'l-aṣwātu) before the Merciful, and thou shalt hear nothing but a whisper"* (Qur'an 20:108)

Verse 3:83 is the doctrinal parallel, and its *ṭawʿan wa karhan* — willingly or unwillingly — states the two modes of the submission verse 87 describes as *dākhirīn*. Everything comes to God humbled; some submitted willingly in life and come humbled in worship; others submitted unwillingly and come humbled in defeat.

Verse 19:95 supplies the individual form of the coming, cited by the source commentary:

> *"And each of them shall come unto Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone (fardan)."* (Qur'an 19:95)

The juxtaposition of *kullun atawhu* (all come to Him) with *fardan* (alone) is one of the Qur'an's most striking combinations: the gathering is total and the arrival is solitary. Verse 83's ranks and verse 87's humbled coming are collective; verse 19:95 states that within the collective each person arrives alone — without wealth, without family, without position, without the company that defined him in life.

**The Terror of the Day: The Qur'an's Vocabulary**

The source commentary lists the other verses describing the terror: 70:11, 79:8, 80:34.

> *"The guilty would wish to ransom himself from the punishment of that Day at the price of his children"* (Qur'an 70:11)

> *"Hearts that Day shall tremble (wājifah), their eyes downcast"* (Qur'an 79:8–9)

> *"That Day when a man will flee from his brother, and his mother and his father, and his wife and his children — for every man of them that Day will have a concern sufficient to occupy him"* (Qur'an 80:34–37)

The passages describe the terror's effects: the willingness to surrender one's children (70:11), the trembling of hearts and downcast eyes (79:8–9), the flight from one's closest relations (80:34–37). Verse 87's *faziʿa* is the general term covering all of them.

Verse 22:1–2 supplies the most famous image:

> *"O mankind! Fear your Lord. Verily the quake of the Hour is a tremendous thing. On the Day when you see it, every nursing woman will forget her nursling, and every pregnant woman will miscarry her burden; and thou shalt see mankind intoxicated, though they are not intoxicated — but the punishment of God is severe."* (Qur'an 22:1–2)

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 87 asks the reader to hold two facts simultaneously: that a terror of universal scope is coming, and that an exception exists by God's will.

The two facts produce the verse's practical instruction, which is not despair but preparation. The terror is real; the exception is real; and the exception is not arbitrary — verse 89 will identify those in it as the ones who brought good deeds, and verse 21:103 as those whom the Greatest Terror does not grieve.

The preparation has three components that the tradition draws from the verse and its parallels.

**Submission in life rather than in defeat.** Verse 3:83's *ṭawʿan wa karhan* presents the two modes of the *dākhirīn* arrival. The person who submits willingly now will arrive humbled in worship; the person who refuses will arrive humbled in defeat, and the humility is unavoidable either way. The choice is not between humility and pride but between two kinds of humility.

**Security through the deed.** Verse 89 will state the ground of the security: *whoever brings a good deed.* The security from the terror is not granted by lineage, position, or claim; it is granted by the deed brought. The report at al-Bukhārī 3688 — *"What have you prepared for it?"* — is the practical form of the preparation.

**Recognition of the exception's sovereignty.** The exception is *man shāʾa Llāh* — whom God wills — and the will is not accessible to calculation. The person who understands this does not assume he is in the exception and does not despair of being in it; he prepares as though the matter depends on his deed and relies as though it depends on God's will, which is exactly the combination verse 79 commanded (*fa-tawakkal ʿalā'Llāh*) and verse 81 defined (*those who believe in Our signs and are submitters*).

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:88

> **And thou seest the mountains that thou dost suppose are solid pass away like clouds—the Work of God, Who perfects all things. Truly He is Aware of whatsoever you do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mountains Like Clouds**

*Wa tarā'l-jibāla taḥsabuhā jāmidatan wa hiya tamurru murra'l-saḥāb; ṣunʿa'Llāhi'lladhī atqana kulla shayʾ; inna Llāha khabīrun bi-mā tafʿalūn* — "And thou seest the mountains — thou supposest them fixed — while they pass as the passing of the clouds. [This is] the workmanship of God, Who has perfected everything. Verily God is Aware of what you do."

Verse 87 described the trumpet and the terror. Verse 88 describes the mountains, and the description is one of the Qur'an's most discussed natural images. The verse has been read eschatologically, cosmologically, and allegorically, and all three readings have classical support.

**Wa Tarā'l-Jibāla: The Address to the Seer**

*"And thou seest the mountains."* The verb *tarā* is second-person singular addressed to the Prophet ﷺ — and through him, in the tradition's reading, to every reader. The address makes the observation personal and immediate: *you see.*

*Al-jibāl* — the mountains — is one of the Qur'an's most frequent natural images, and the mountains function in the revelation as the paradigm of stability, permanence, and immovability:

> *"And He has cast into the earth firm mountains (rawāsiya) lest it shake with you"* (Qur'an 16:15; 21:31; 31:10)

> *"And the mountains as pegs (awtādan)"* (Qur'an 78:7)

> *"And He has set in the earth firm mountains above it, and blessed it, and measured therein its sustenance in four days"* (Qur'an 41:10)

The Qur'an's presentation of the mountains as *rawāsiy* — anchored, firm — and *awtād* — pegs, stakes — establishes them as the symbols of what does not move. Verse 88 takes precisely this symbol and states that it moves.

**Taḥsabuhā Jāmidatan: "Thou Supposest Them Fixed"**

*"Thou supposest them solid."* *Taḥsabuhā* is from *ḥ-s-b*, to reckon, to suppose, to compute — the verb of calculation and estimation, used in verse 63 of the objectors' reckoning and in 18:36 of the garden-owner's supposition that the Hour would not come.

*Jāmidah* is from *j-m-d*, to be frozen, solid, immobile, congealed — the same root as *jamad*, ice, and *jāmid*, motionless. The word denotes not merely stillness but the stillness of a solid body: fixed, rigid, without motion.

The verse therefore sets up an explicit contrast between what the observer calculates (*taḥsabuhā jāmidatan*) and what is actually the case (*wa hiya tamurru*). The verb of the first clause is the verb of human estimation; the verb of the second is the verb of actual motion.

**Wa Hiya Tamurru Murra'l-Saḥāb: "While They Pass as the Passing of the Clouds"**

The clause is the verse's central claim. *Tamurru* is from *m-r-r*, to pass, to go by, to move along — the verb of transit. *Murra'l-saḥāb* — "the passing of the clouds" — is a cognate accusative (*mafʿūl muṭlaq*) specifying the manner: they pass *with the passing of clouds*, cloud-like in their transit.

*Al-saḥāb* — the clouds — appeared at verse 63 of this sūrah in the phrase *al-ṣawāʾifa'l-bushrā* — no; the sūrah's cloud reference is at verse 63's *bi'l-riyāḥi bushran* (the winds as glad tidings) and the clouds are the Qur'an's standard image of movement that is real but unhurried, visible but weightless:

> *"And God is He Who sends the winds, and they raise a cloud, and He drives it unto a dead land"* (Qur'an 35:9)

> *"And thou seest the clouds heavy (thiqālan)"* — and: *"And it is He Who sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy, and We send down from the sky pure water"* (Qur'an 7:57; 25:48; 30:46)

> *"Dost thou not see that God drives the clouds (yuzjī'l-saḥāb), then joins them together, then makes them a heap, and thou seest the rain come forth from within them?"* (Qur'an 24:43)

The comparison is precise: the mountains move as clouds move — continuously, in a manner not apparent to the observer, in a direction determined by something other than themselves.

**The Two Principal Interpretations**

The source commentary records two readings, and the tradition has discussed them at length.

**The cosmological reading**: some commentators interpret *pass away* as "move" and *thou dost suppose are solid* to mean that mountains move but we cannot see this movement, since large objects viewed from a distance, even if they are moving quickly, appear to be standing still (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī).

The reading takes the verse as a statement about the present world: the mountains are moving now, and their apparent fixity is an artifact of scale and distance. Al-Rāzī's argument is the analogy of a distant object — a large ship seen from afar appears stationary while moving rapidly, and a cloud layer seen from a great distance appears fixed while traveling at speed.

The reading has been widely discussed in modern times in connection with the earth's motion and the movement of the continents, and the proponents of scientific exegesis (*al-tafsīr al-ʿilmī*) cite it as a Qur'anic indication of terrestrial motion. The traditional scholarly position on such readings is cautious: the verse permits the reading, and whether it intends it is not established. Al-Rāzī himself, who proposed the analogy, did not claim to demonstrate the earth's motion from the verse; he offered it as an explanation of how a fixed-appearing thing could move.

**The eschatological reading**: others interpret the whole phrase as referring to an event at the end of the world similar to the destruction or movement of mountains at the Hour, spoken of in other verses (al-Rāzī), and the source commentary lists them: 18:47, 19:90, 20:105, 52:10, 56:5, 69:14, 70:9, 73:14, 77:10, 78:20, 81:3, 101:5.

> *"And on the Day We cause the mountains to pass away (nusayyiru'l-jibāla), and thou seest the earth a level plain"* (Qur'an 18:47)

> *"And the mountains will be as carded wool (al-ʿihni'l-manfūsh)"* (Qur'an 101:5)

> *"And the mountains are made to move (suyyirat)"* (Qur'an 81:3)

> *"And they ask thee concerning the mountains. Say: 'My Lord will scatter them as dust (yansifuhā rabbī nassfā); and He will leave them a level plain'"* (Qur'an 20:105–106)

> *"And the mountains shall be set in motion, and shall become a mirage"* (Qur'an 78:20)

> *"And the day when the heaven shakes with a shaking, and the mountains move with a moving"* (Qur'an 52:9–10)

> *"And the mountains are crushed with a single crushing"* (Qur'an 69:14)

The eschatological reading takes verse 88 as continuing verse 87's scene: at the trumpet-blow, the mountains — the symbols of permanence — pass like clouds. Verse 87's *yawma* (on the Day) governs, and verse 88 describes what the seer will see on that Day.

The majority of classical commentators prefer the eschatological reading, primarily because of the verse's position between the trumpet (87) and the accounting (89–90). Ibn Kathīr holds it, and al-Ṭabarī inclines to it. The cosmological reading is recorded and defended by al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī, and it has had considerable modern currency.

**Two Further Readings**

The source commentary records two additional interpretations.

**The earth itself**: some interpret *pass away* as a reference to the earth itself, meaning that the world, believed to be solid, is actually as ephemeral as a cloud (al-Qurṭubī).

**The allegorical reading**: another interpretation is that this verse is an allegory for the spirit when it leaves the body at death and rises toward the Divine Throne (al-Qurṭubī).

The allegorical reading is accompanied in the source commentary by an anecdote of considerable interest: *it is said that when the famous Sufi Junayd al-Baghdādī was asked why he did not appear to have the same visible states and ecstasies as other Sufis, he responded by reciting this verse.*

Junayd's use of the verse is the Sufi reading of the mountains: the great thing that appears fixed is in continuous motion, and its motion is invisible to the observer. The person whose interior is in constant movement toward God may appear externally immobile, exactly as the mountains appear *jāmidah* while passing like clouds. The anecdote is a lesson in the relation of the outward to the inward, and it connects verse 88 to verse 74 — *thy Lord knows what their breasts conceal and what they disclose.*

**Ṣunʿa'Llāhi'lladhī Atqana Kulla Shayʾ: The Workmanship of God**

*"[This is] the workmanship of God, Who perfects all things."* *Ṣunʿa* is from *ṣ-n-ʿ*, to make, to manufacture, to craft — the same root as *ṣāniʿ*, maker, *ṣināʿah*, craft, and *muṣṭanaʿ*, made for a purpose. Verse 38 of this sūrah used the term of the throne: *unẓurū ilā ʿarshihā* — and verse 20:39 of Moses: *wa li-tuṣnaʿa ʿalā ʿaynī* — "and that thou mightest be made under My eye" — using the same root for the crafting of a prophet.

The word *ṣunʿa* denotes crafted work, and its application to the movement of the mountains describes that movement as *manufacture* — not accident, not decay, but skilled making.

*Atqana* is from *ṭ-q-n*, to be skilled, proficient, masterful, precise — the same root as *itqān*, mastery, and *mutqin*, one who does a thing precisely. The source commentary records the interpretations: *perfects* renders *atqana*, which some understand to mean "to tie down" or "make firm" (al-Ṭabarī), or which in this context means that God "ties together" things according to His Wisdom (al-Rāzī).

The dual sense — firmness and skill — is characteristic of the root, and al-Ṭabarī's "tie down" connects *atqana* to *rawāsiy* (the anchored mountains): God has made firm and has made skillfully, and the firmness itself is the skill.

The Qur'an's parallel statements:

> *"And thou seest the mountains — thou supposest them fixed — while they pass as the passing of the clouds. The workmanship of God, Who has perfected (atqana) everything"* (Qur'an 27:88)

> *"The making of God, Who has perfected (atqana) everything"* — and: *"He Who created, then fashioned, then proportioned (fa-sawwā)"* (Qur'an 87:2)

> *"Verily We created everything by measure (bi-qadar)"* (Qur'an 54:49)

> *"Who has perfected (aḥsana) everything He created"* (Qur'an 32:7)

> *"Glory be to God, the best of creators"* — and: *"And We created man in the best of forms"* (Qur'an 23:14; 95:4)

Verse 32:7's *aḥsana* and verse 27:88's *atqana* are the Qur'an's two terms for the perfection of the creation, and the pairing appears at verse 15 of this sūrah in the phrase *ʿalīmun ḥakīm* — knowing, wise — which is the source of the perfection.

**Inna Llāha Khabīrun Bi-Mā Tafʿalūn: The Concluding Awareness**

*"Truly He is Aware of whatsoever you do."* *Khabīr* is from *kh-b-r*, to be acquainted with, to know by experience, to be aware of the inner reality of a thing — the same root as *khibrah*, experiential knowledge, and *khabar*, report. The Name *al-Khabīr* denotes knowledge of the interior and the particular.

The shift from the third person (*tafʿalūn* — what you do, plural) to the general statement is significant. The verse has described the mountains and the crafting of God; it ends with God's awareness of the reader's actions. The connection is the verse's argument: the One who crafted the mountains with precision is aware of what you do with the same precision.

The Qur'an uses the same formula repeatedly:

> *"And God is Aware of what you do (khabīrun bi-mā taʿmalūn)"* (Qur'an 2:234, 271; 3:153; 5:8; 8:72; 49:13; 62:11; 64:8; 100:11)

> *"And He is with you wherever you are; and God sees what you do"* (Qur'an 57:4)

> *"Verily God is not unaware of what you do"* — cf. 2:74, 85, 140, 144, 149; 3:99

Verse 62:11 uses the formula in a context of accountability, and verse 100:11 uses it of the Day:

> *"Verily their Lord, on that Day, of them will be Aware (la-khabīr)."* (Qur'an 100:11)

Verse 100:11 is the eschatological form of verse 88's conclusion, and it places the awareness on the Day — which is where verse 88's context, following verse 87, already is.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 88 has three applications corresponding to its three readings, and each is available to the reader without resolving which the verse intends.

**The permanence that is not permanent.** The mountains are the symbol of what lasts, and verse 88 states that they move and will pass. The application is to everything the reader treats as a mountain: his position, his property, his body, his family's standing, his country, his civilization. Verse 34 of this sūrah described the kings entering towns and corrupting them; verse 49 described the plotters' houses falling empty; verse 52 described the houses of the Thamūd as empty for their wrongdoing; verse 58 described the rained-upon city. The mountains of human life pass like clouds, and the person who has built on them has built on a moving thing.

The tradition's discipline on this point is *zuhd* — detachment — and its ground is precisely verse 88: not that the world is evil but that it is *jāmidah* in appearance and *mārrah* in fact.

**The unseen motion of the great things.** The cosmological reading gives the verse a second application: much of what appears fixed is in motion, and the motion is invisible because of scale. The principle applies to institutions, traditions, communities, and hearts. A marriage that appears fixed may be drifting; a community that appears stable may be changing; a heart that appears settled may be hardening — verse 66's descent from knowledge stopping short to doubt to blindness operates while the person believes nothing is moving.

The practical instruction is the one verse 69 gave: travel and look. The observer who examines the great apparently-fixed things — his own life included — over time discovers the motion, and verse 88 says the discovery is a *ṣunʿa*, a crafted work of God, not an accident.

**The interior motion invisible to others.** Junayd's use of the verse gives the third application: the person whose interior is in continuous movement toward God may appear externally still, and the appearance is not a report on the reality. Verse 74 established that the breasts' concealment is known; verse 88 states that the apparent fixity of great things is unreliable; and verse 40 established that God is not unaware of the hidden.

The application runs in both directions. The reader should not judge his own spiritual state by its visible manifestations — the absence of ecstasy is not the absence of motion. And he should not judge another's by its visible stillness. Verse 88's mountain that passes like clouds is the Qur'an's image for the reality that exceeds its appearance, and the appearance is what human beings judge by.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:89

> **Whosoever brings a good deed, he will have that which is better than it. And that Day they will be secure from terror.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Principle of the Good Deed**

*Man jāʾa bi'l-ḥasanati fa-lahū khayrun minhā wa hum min fazaʿin yawmaʾidhin āminūn* — "Whoever brings a good deed shall have that which is better than it, and they from terror on that Day are secure."

Verse 88 described the passing of the mountains. Verse 89 states what will remain when they have passed: the deed a person brings. The verse is al-Naml's statement of the law of recompense, and it is one of the Qur'an's most consoling single sentences.

**Man Jāʾa Bi'l-Ḥasanah: "Whoever Brings a Good Deed"**

*"Whoever brings a good deed."* *Jāʾa* — brings, comes — is the verb of arrival, and the construction presents the person as arriving *with* the deed. The image is of a traveler reaching a destination carrying what he has gathered, and the Qur'an uses it consistently:

> *"And everyone comes to Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone"* (Qur'an 19:95 — quoted at v. 87)

> *"And We shall present for them a Book which he shall find wide open"* (Qur'an 17:13–14)

> *"On that Day mankind will issue in scattered groups to be shown their deeds"* (Qur'an 99:6)

*Al-ḥasanah* — the good deed — is from *ḥ-s-n*, goodness, beauty, excellence; the same root as *ḥusn*, beauty, *aḥsan*, best, *muḥsin*, one who does good, and *iḥsān*, excellence. The Qur'an's *ḥasanah* covers the full range of good: belief, worship, charity, kindness, honesty, patience, and the acts of the heart.

The source commentary records the specific identification: *a good deed* refers to testifying "There is no god but God" and believing in Him (al-Qurṭubī); and al-Qurṭubī quotes a ḥadīth stating *"Lā ilāha illa'Llāh is the best of good things."*

The identification is not restrictive but foundational: the *ḥasanah* that verse 89 primarily names is the testimony of faith, because it is the deed that gives value to every other deed. Verse 18:110 states the connection:

> *"So whoever hopes for the meeting of his Lord, let him work righteous deeds and associate none in the worship of his Lord"* (Qur'an 18:110)

The righteous deed (*ʿamalan ṣāliḥan*) requires the non-association, and the non-association is *lā ilāha illa'Llāh*. Verse 24:39 supplies the negative case: the deeds of those who disbelieve are as a mirage, and the thirsty man finds nothing.

**Fa-Lahū Khayrun Minhā: "He Shall Have That Which Is Better Than It"**

The clause is the verse's central promise, and its grammar is remarkable. *Khayrun minhā* — "better than it" — makes the reward *better than the deed itself*, not merely equal to it or a multiple of it.

The source commentary records the interpretive range: *which is better* can mean many things, among them knowledge of God in this world, the vision of God in the Hereafter (al-Rāzī), or the Contentment of God (al-Qurṭubī). It can also refer to the multiplication of good deeds as mentioned in 6:160: *whoever brings a good deed shall have ten times the like thereof* (al-Qurṭubī). For some this verse implies that people of faith, if they are punished in the Hereafter, will not be punished forever (al-Rāzī). Some say that *what is better* refers to being secure from terror on that day (al-Ṭabarī).

The readings are complementary rather than competing, and each identifies a different aspect of the "better":

**Multiplication**: the reward exceeds the deed in quantity.

> *"Whoever brings a good deed shall have ten times the like thereof; and whoever brings an evil deed shall be recompensed only with the like thereof, and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 6:160)

> *"The parable of those who spend their wealth in the way of God is as the parable of a grain that grows seven ears, in every ear a hundred grains. And God multiplies for whom He wills; and God is Embracing, Knowing."* (Qur'an 2:261)

Verse 6:160 states the minimum multiplication (tenfold) and the asymmetry with evil (matched only), and verse 2:261 states the maximum (seven hundredfold) with the qualification *and God multiplies for whom He wills* — the multiplication is not capped.

**Quality**: the reward exceeds the deed in kind.

> *"No soul knows what is kept hidden for them of the coolness of eyes, as a recompense for what they used to do."* (Qur'an 32:17)

> *"And therein is what the souls desire and what the eyes delight in, and you shall abide therein"* (Qur'an 43:71)

> *"And We shall remove from their breasts any [sense of] injury — [they will be] as brothers, upon thrones facing one another"* (Qur'an 15:47)

Verse 32:17's *lā taʿlamu nafs* — no soul knows — states that the reward exceeds human comprehension, which is the strongest form of "better than."

**The vision of God**: al-Rāzī's reading identifies the *khayr* with the beatific vision, which is the highest reward in the tradition's theology.

> *"Faces on that Day shall be radiant (nāḍirah), looking upon their Lord (ilā rabbihā nāẓirah)"* (Qur'an 75:22–23)

The ḥadīth literature develops the point extensively:

> *"You shall see your Lord as you see the full moon, without difficulty in seeing Him."* (al-Bukhārī 554; Muslim 633)

> *"'Is there anything better than what we have?' He said: 'The Pleasure of God (riḍwānu Llāh) is greater.'"* — and the report at al-Bukhārī 6549 and Muslim 2829 in which God asks the inhabitants of the Garden whether they wish for more, and they say: "Have You not whitened our faces, admitted us to the Garden, and saved us from the Fire?" Then the veil is lifted, and nothing given to them is more beloved than looking at their Lord.

**The Contentment of God**: al-Qurṭubī's reading identifies the *khayr* with *riḍwān*, God's good pleasure.

> *"God has promised the believers, men and women, gardens beneath which rivers flow... and the Pleasure of God (wa riḍwānun mina Llāh) is greater. That is the great triumph."* (Qur'an 9:72)

Verse 9:72 states the hierarchy explicitly: the gardens, the rivers, the dwellings, and then — *and the Pleasure of God is greater* — *akbar*. The *riḍwān* is the *khayr minhā* of verse 89.

**Security from the terror**: al-Ṭabarī's reading connects the *khayr* to the second clause of the verse.

**The implication for the sinful believer**: al-Rāzī's reading, recorded in the source commentary, is that the verse implies people of faith, if punished in the Hereafter, will not be punished forever. The argument is that the one who brings a *ḥasanah* — which is the testimony of faith — receives *khayr minhā*, and eternal punishment is not *khayr*; therefore the one who brought the *ḥasanah* cannot be punished eternally.

The reading is the classical Sunni position on the sinful believer: he may be punished for his sins, and his punishment is finite; the *ḥasanah* of faith prevents its perpetuity. The ḥadīth literature is explicit:

> *"Whoever says 'lā ilāha illa'Llāh' sincerely shall enter the Garden."* — and: *"He whose last words are 'lā ilāha illa'Llāh' shall enter the Garden."* (Abū Dāwūd 3116; Aḥmad, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

> *"No one who has an atom's weight of faith in his heart shall enter the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 44; Muslim 193)

> *"God will bring forth from the Fire people in whose hearts was an atom's weight of faith."* (al-Bukhārī 7439; Muslim 183 — the report of the intercession and the exit from the Fire)

**Wa Hum Min Fazaʿin Yawmaʾidhin Āminūn: "And They From Terror Are Secure"**

The second clause specifies one content of the *khayr*. *Fazaʿ* — terror — is the same root as verse 87's *faziʿa* (were terrified) and 21:103's *al-fazaʿ al-akbar* (the Greatest Terror). *Āminūn* is from *ʾ-m-n*, security, safety — the same root as *īmān* (faith) and *amān* (safety), and the connection is doctrinally significant: faith (*īmān*) produces security (*amn*).

Verse 6:82 states the connection in its classical form:

> *"Those who believe and confound not their belief with wrong — theirs is security (al-amn), and they are guided."* (Qur'an 6:82)

> *"And He will surely replace for them, after their fear, security (amnan)* — *[because] they worship Me, associating nothing with Me"* (Qur'an 24:55)

Verse 24:55's *min baʿdi khawfihim amnā* — security after their fear — is the worldly form of verse 89's promise, and its ground is the same: worship without association.

Verse 21:103 supplies the eschatological form:

> *"The Greatest Terror will not grieve them, and the angels will meet them: 'This is your Day which you were promised.'"* (Qur'an 21:103)

Verse 89's *min fazaʿin āminūn* is the identical promise in al-Naml's terms, and the terror named is the terror of verse 87 — the terror at the trumpet-blow. The sequence is exact: at the blow, all in the heavens and earth are terrified *save whom God wills* (v. 87); and whoever brings a good deed is *secure from terror on that Day* (v. 89). Verse 89 identifies the exception of verse 87.

**The Verse's Placement Between the Mountain and the Fire**

Verse 89 stands between verse 88's passing mountains and verse 90's faces cast into the Fire, and the placement produces one of the Qur'an's most characteristic rhythms: the statement of the general dissolution, then the two outcomes, then the assurance.

The mountains pass (88). The good deed remains and receives better (89). The evil deed receives its like (90). The reader is left with the only thing that survives the dissolution of the mountains, and the thing is his deed.

Verse 18:46 makes the same point in a single sentence:

> *"Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world; but the abiding good deeds (al-bāqiyātu'l-ṣāliḥāt) are better with thy Lord for reward and better for hope."* (Qur'an 18:46)

> *"And the Day when the heaven rolls up like a scroll"* — and: *"Wealth will not avail"* — cf. 104:2–3

The *bāqiyāt ṣāliḥāt* — the abiding good deeds — are what remain when the mountains pass, and verse 89 promises that what remains receives *khayr minhā*.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 89 asks the reader what he will bring, and the question is answerable now.

**Identify the ḥasanah.** The tradition's identification — the testimony of faith — establishes the foundation, and the foundation is available to anyone who makes it sincerely. But verse 89's *man jāʾa bi'l-ḥasanah* is indefinite: *a* good deed, any good deed. Verse 99:7 confirms the range: *whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it.* The reader does not need a great deed; he needs a good one, brought.

**Understand the "better."** The reward is not equal to the deed but better than it, and the betterness operates in quantity (6:160's tenfold), in kind (32:17's unknown coolness of eyes), and in quality (9:72's Pleasure of God, which is greater). The person who gives a coin receives more than a coin; the person who speaks a good word receives more than the word's effect; the person who restrains his anger receives more than the restraint.

Verse 55:60 states the principle as a question: *Is the reward of goodness (iḥsān) anything but goodness (iḥsān)?* — and the answer is that the reward is goodness, which is to say, better.

**Recognize the security as the primary content.** Al-Ṭabarī's reading identifies the *khayr* with the security from terror, and the identification is the verse's practical point. The terror of verse 87 is universal and the security of verse 89 is specific; the security is not the absence of the Day but the absence of the Day's terror for the one who brought the deed.

Verse 15:97–99, quoted at verse 70 of this sūrah, prescribes the practice that produces the security: *glorify with the praise of thy Lord, be among those who prostrate, and worship thy Lord until certainty comes.* The worship is the bringing of the *ḥasanah*, and the certainty (*yaqīn*) is what verse 82 said mankind lacked.

Verse 89's promise is therefore the sūrah's answer to verse 82's reproach: the person who is certain of the signs, who believes in them (v. 81), and who brings the good deed will be secure from the terror whose absence of certainty produced the beast's speech.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:90

> **And whosoever brings an evil deed, their faces shall be cast down into the Fire. Are you recompensed for aught save that which you used to do?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Principle of the Evil Deed**

*Wa man jāʾa bi'l-sayyiʾati fa-kubbat wujūhuhum fī'l-nār; hal tujzawna illā mā kuntum taʿmalūn* — "And whoever brings an evil deed — their faces shall be cast down into the Fire. 'Are you recompensed for aught save that which you used to do?'"

Verse 89 stated the law of the good deed. Verse 90 states the law of the evil deed, and the two verses form a pair that closes al-Naml's eschatological argument. The verse has three components: the bringing of the evil deed, the casting of the faces into the Fire, and the question that interprets both.

**Wa Man Jāʾa Bi'l-Sayyiʾah: "Whoever Brings an Evil Deed"**

The construction parallels verse 89 exactly: *man jāʾa bi-* — whoever brings. The verb of arrival is the same; the object differs.

*Al-sayyiʾah* — the evil deed — is from *s-w-ʾ*, evil, badness, harm; the same root as *sūʾ*, evil, and *sāʾa*, to be evil, and *masāwī*, faults. The Qur'an uses the term for the bad deed in its full range:

> *"And whoever brings an evil deed (wa man jāʾa bi'l-sayyiʾah) shall be recompensed only with the like thereof"* (Qur'an 6:160; 28:84)

> *"And whoever brings an evil deed — those shall be cast down into the Fire upon their faces"* — cf. 27:90

> *"And the recompense of an evil deed is an evil the like thereof; but whoever pardons and reconciles, his reward is with God"* (Qur'an 42:40)

The source commentary records the specific identification: *an evil deed* refers to idolatry (*shirk*) (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī).

The identification parallels verse 89's identification of the *ḥasanah* as the testimony of faith, and the two identifications state the same doctrine from opposite poles: the decisive deed is the one concerning God's oneness. Affirming it is the *ḥasanah* that receives better; denying it by association is the *sayyiʾah* that receives the Fire.

The Qur'an states the exclusivity of *shirk* as the unforgivable deed:

> *"Verily God forgives not that anything be associated with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills"* (Qur'an 4:48, 116)

> *"And whoever associates anything with God — he has strayed with a far straying"* (Qur'an 4:116)

> *"Verily association is a great wrong"* (Qur'an 31:13 — quoted at v. 85)

The identification does not exclude the general reading, in which *sayyiʾah* covers every evil deed; the classical commentators hold both, with *shirk* as the paradigm case and the greatest instance.

Verse 6:160 and 28:84 state the recompense's measure: *the like thereof* — *mithluhā*. The evil deed is not multiplied; it is matched. The asymmetry with the good deed — multiplied tenfold to seven hundredfold, matched for evil — is the Qur'an's clearest statement of the mercy's precedence over the justice, and verse 73 of this sūrah supplied its ground: *thy Lord is Possessed of Bounty toward mankind.*

**Fa-Kubbat Wujūhuhum Fī'l-Nār: "Their Faces Shall Be Cast Down Into the Fire"**

The clause is the verse's most vivid and most discussed. *Kubbat* is the passive of *kabba*, Form I, to cast down headlong, to throw on the face, to overturn — the verb of a violent downward casting in which the head goes first.

The Qur'an uses the same verb in a related construction:

> *"And We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces (ʿalā wujūhihim) — blind, dumb, deaf"* (Qur'an 17:97)

> *"Is he who walks cast down upon his face (yamsyī mukibban ʿalā wajhihī) more rightly guided, or he who walks upright upon a straight path?"* (Qur'an 67:22)

> *"The Day when they are dragged upon their faces into the Fire (yusḥabūna fī'l-nāri ʿalā wujūhihim): 'Taste the touch of the Blaze!'"* (Qur'an 54:48)

> *"And they shall be cast into it"* — and: *"Whenever a group is cast into it"* — cf. 67:8; 33:66

Verse 67:22 is the verse's most important parallel, and it supplies the interpretive key: the person cast down upon his face is contrasted with the person walking upright on a straight path. The casting is not merely a manner of punishment but the culmination of a manner of walking — the one who moved through life head-down, unable to see where he went, arrives head-down in the Fire.

The source commentary records the imagery parallels: 17:97, 18:29, 23:104, 33:66, 54:48. It notes: *here "faces shall be cast down" can refer to being cast into the Fire upside down as in 17:97, or dragged upon their faces as in 54:48. According to some the face, like the head or neck, can in idiomatic usage stand for the whole person, so that "their faces" means simply "they"* (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī).

**The Face as the Person**

The three readings of *wujūhuhum* deserve attention because the verse's severity depends on which is taken.

**Literal and positional**: they are cast in headfirst, upside down, on their faces — 17:97's *ʿalā wujūhihim* and 54:48's dragged upon their faces.

**Idiomatic**: the face stands for the whole person (as the head or neck does in Arabic idiom), and *their faces shall be cast down* means simply *they shall be cast down* (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī).

**Symbolic**: the face is the seat of dignity, of identity, of the front that a person presents to the world; the casting of the face is the casting of the dignity.

The three readings are not alternatives in the tradition's practice; they accumulate. The person is cast headfirst (positional), the whole person is cast (idiomatic), and his dignity is cast with him (symbolic). Verse 75:22–24 supplies the contrast:

> *"Faces on that Day shall be radiant (wujūhun yawmaʾidhin nāḍirah), looking upon their Lord"* (Qur'an 75:22–23)

> *"And faces on that Day shall be downcast (wujūhun yawmaʾidhin bāsirah), upon them is dust, and it is covered by darkness"* (Qur'an 80:40–41)

> *"And faces on that Day shall be covered with dust, overcast by a gloom"* (Qur'an 80:40–41)

Verse 75:22's radiant faces and verse 90's cast-down faces are the two outcomes of verse 89 and verse 90 respectively, and the Qur'an's eschatology frequently uses the face as the indicator of the whole person's state.

**Hal Tujzawna Illā Mā Kuntum Taʿmalūn: "Are You Recompensed For Aught Save What You Used to Do?"**

The question is addressed to those cast into the Fire, and the source commentary records the interpretive note: *for some the question can also be read as a description of what will be said to the people as they are cast down into the Fire* (al-Rāzī).

*Tujzawna* is the passive of Form IV *jāzā*, to recompense, to reward, to requite — the same root as *jazāʾ*, recompense, and *juziya*, to be requited. The construction *hal ... illā* — "is it anything but" — is a rhetorical question establishing a limitation: you are recompensed *only* by what you used to do.

The Qur'an uses the identical formula repeatedly:

> *"And they will not be wronged"* — and: *"Are you recompensed save what you used to do?"* (Qur'an 52:16; 37:39; 36:54; 46:19)

> *"That Day every soul shall be recompensed for what it earned; no injustice that Day"* (Qur'an 40:17)

> *"And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another"* (Qur'an 6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38)

> *"And that man has only what he strives for, and that his striving shall be seen, then he shall be recompensed with the fullest recompense"* (Qur'an 53:39–41)

Verse 52:16 is the closest parallel:

> *"Enter it and burn therein; and be patient or be not patient — it is the same for you. You are recompensed only for what you used to do (innamā tujzawna mā kuntum taʿmalūn)."* (Qur'an 52:16)

Verse 37:39 gives the same formula in the negative:

> *"That is the justice of God; He judges between them with justice"* — and: *"You are not recompensed save what you used to do (wa mā tujzawna illā mā kuntum taʿmalūn)"* (Qur'an 37:39)

**The Question's Function: The Elimination of Injustice**

The question is not a taunt, and the tradition reads it as a statement of justice. Its function is to establish that the punishment is exactly proportionate to the deed, and that nothing has been added.

Verse 89 said the good deed receives *better than it* — an excess of mercy. Verse 90 says the evil deed receives *only what you used to do* — an exactness of justice. The two statements together define the Qur'an's economy of recompense: mercy multiplies, justice matches, and neither wrongs.

Verse 10:44 states the principle in its most complete form:

> *"Verily God does not wrong people an atom's weight; but people wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 10:44)

> *"And thy Lord wrongs not the servants"* (Qur'an 50:29 — quoted at v. 11 of this sūrah)

Verse 18:49 gave the criminals' own acknowledgment of the exactness:

> *"Woe unto us! What a Book is this! It omits not a small thing nor a great, but has numbered it. And they shall find what they did present. And thy Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

The acknowledgment *they shall find what they did present* — *wa wajadū mā ʿamilūu ḥāḍiran* — is verse 90's question answered by the accused themselves: the recompense is what they did, present, found, numbered.

**Kuntum Taʿmalūn: The Past Habitual**

*"What you used to do."* The construction — *kāna* with the imperfect — denotes habitual or continuous past action, and the same construction appeared at verse 84 (*ammādhā kuntum taʿmalūn* — what were you used to doing?).

The habitual aspect is essential to the verse's justice. The recompense is not for an isolated act but for a pattern of doing, and the pattern was formed by repeated choice over time. Verse 85's ground — *bi-mā ẓalamū* — named the wrong; verse 90's *kuntum taʿmalūn* names its continuity.

The Qur'an states the formation of the pattern:

> *"And your Lord creates what He wills and chooses"* — and: *"Nay, but their hearts are covered (ghulf) for what they used to earn"* (Qur'an 2:88)

> *"So when they turned away, God turned their hearts away"* (Qur'an 61:5 — quoted at v. 80)

> *"Nay, but the rust (rān) has covered their hearts, from what they used to earn"* (Qur'an 83:14)

Verse 83:14's *rān* — rust, covering — is the Qur'an's image for the accumulation produced by repeated evil doing, and the accumulation is what verse 90 recompenses. The person cast into the Fire is cast for the pattern he built, and the pattern was built by choices each of which was individually available to reverse.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 90 has three applications, and they are the same three that verse 89 had in positive form.

**What you bring is what you built.** The person arriving at verse 90 arrives with a *sayyiʾah* — an evil deed — and the deed is the crystallization of a habitual pattern. The verse's warning is therefore about the pattern rather than the single act: the act can be repented (verse 11's model of Moses: *ẓalamtu nafsī, faghfir lī, faghafara lah*); the pattern, formed by unrepented repetition, becomes the rust of verse 83:14.

The practical instruction is the one the tradition derives from the report at al-Bukhārī 6502 and Muslim 2767:

> *"God spreads out His Hand at night that the sinner of the day may repent, and He spreads out His Hand by day that the sinner of the night may repent, until the sun rises from its place of setting."* (Muslim 2759)

The report connects to verse 82's sign — the sun's rising from the west closes the repentance — and to verse 86's night and day: the two periods the verse named as signs are the two periods in which repentance is available.

**The face is the person.** The casting of the faces is the casting of the dignity, and the dignity is lost by the deed. The worldly analogue is familiar: the person who lies loses the ability to be believed; the person who betrays loses the ability to be trusted; the person who takes what is not his loses the standing of one whose word counts. The face is what a person presents, and the presentation is formed by the deeds.

Verse 75:22–23 supplies the alternative: faces radiant, looking upon their Lord. The radiance is not cosmetic; it is the visible form of a life in which the deeds were good, and verse 89's *ḥasanah* is its ground.

**The recompense is not arbitrary.** Verse 90's question eliminates the most common objection to divine punishment — that it is disproportionate or unjust. The answer is that the recompense is *what you used to do*, no more. The person who objects to the punishment is objecting to the description of his own pattern.

That is the verse's final severity and its final mercy. The severity is that the punishment is exact and unavoidable. The mercy is that it was avoidable at every point in the pattern's formation, and that verse 89 states the alternative — whoever brings a good deed shall have better than it — in the sentence immediately before.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:91

> **Truly I am commanded to worship the Lord of this city, Who has made it sacred. And unto Him belong all things. And I am commanded to be among those who submit.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing Declaration**

*Innamā umirtu an aʿbuda rabba hādhihi'l-baladati'lladhī ḥarramahā wa lahū kullu shayʾ; wa umirtu an akūna mina'l-muslimīn* — "I have only been commanded to worship the Lord of this city, Who has made it sacred, and to Whom belongs everything; and I have been commanded to be among those who submit."

Verse 90 concluded the eschatological argument. Verse 91 begins the sūrah's coda — three verses (91–93) in which the Prophet ﷺ is commanded to speak in the first person, stating his own commission, his own task, and his own assurance. The shift from the third-person narration of verses 82–90 to the first-person declaration of verses 91–93 is the sūrah's final movement, and it ends where al-Naml began: with the Prophet ﷺ and the revelation given to him.

**Innamā Umirtu: "I Have Only Been Commanded"**

*Innamā* — "only, merely" — is the particle of restriction (*ḥaṣr*), combining *inna* (verily) with *mā* (not), and it produces the meaning "nothing but." Its use here restricts the Prophet's ﷺ commission to what follows: I have been commanded *only* to worship.

The restriction has a specific rhetorical function. Verse 92 will state the second restriction — *I am but a warner* (innamā anā mina'l-mundhirīn) — and the two *innamā* constructions together define the office negatively as well as positively: the Prophet ﷺ is commanded to worship and to warn, and he is *not* commanded to compel, to judge, to control the outcome, or to determine who believes.

Verses 80–81 established the limits; verse 91 states the commission within them. The structure is the Qur'an's consistent presentation of prophetic office:

> *"Remind; thou art but a reminder. Thou art not a controller over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

> *"The Messenger's duty is only the proclamation (al-balāgh)"* (Qur'an 5:99)

> *"Not upon thee is their guidance; but God guides whom He wills"* (Qur'an 2:272)

> *"Say: 'I am but a man like you, revealed unto me that your god is one God. So whoever hopes for the meeting of his Lord, let him work righteous deeds and associate none in the worship of his Lord.'"* (Qur'an 18:110)

Verse 18:110 is the closest structural parallel: the declaration of the prophet's humanity, the content of the revelation (one God), and the practical conclusion (righteous deed without association). Verse 91 performs the same three moves in al-Naml's idiom.

**An Aʿbuda: "To Worship"**

*"To worship."* *Aʿbuda* is from *ʿ-b-d*, worship, servitude — the same root as *ʿabd*, servant, *ʿibādah*, worship, and *ʿabīd*, servants. Verse 11 of this sūrah quoted the divine statement *wa mā ana ẓallāmun li'l-ʿabīd* — "and I am not unjust toward the servants" — using the same root in the passive position; verse 91 uses it in the active: *I have been commanded to worship.*

The Qur'an's central statement of the command:

> *"And I created not the jinn and mankind save that they might worship Me (illā li-yaʿbudūn)"* (Qur'an 51:56)

> *"And We sent not before thee any messenger but that We revealed unto him: 'There is no god save Me, so worship Me (fa-ʿbudūn)'"* (Qur'an 21:25)

> *"O mankind! Worship your Lord (uʿbudū rabbakum), Who created you and those before you, that haply you may be Godfearing"* (Qur'an 2:21)

> *"And thy Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him (allā taʿbudū illā iyyāhu)"* (Qur'an 17:23)

Verse 21:25 is the verse that places the command in the mouth of every messenger, and verse 91 is its instance in the mouth of the last. The worship commanded is identical across the whole prophetic chain — *lā ilāha illa'Llāh* — and verse 91 states it as the Prophet's ﷺ own commission rather than as a demand on others.

The tradition's definition of *ʿibādah* is comprehensive. Ibn Taymiyyah in *al-ʿUbūdiyyah* defines it as a term comprehending everything God loves and is pleased with, of words and deeds, outward and inward — *ismun jāmiʿun li-kulli mā yuḥibbuhu Llāhu wa yarḍāhu min al-aqwāl wa'l-aʿmāl al-bāṭinah wa'l-ẓāhirah*. The definition includes belief, prayer, charity, honesty, kindness, patience, and the acts of the heart, and it makes verse 91's command comprehensive rather than ritual.

**Rabba Hādhihi'l-Baladah: "The Lord of This City"**

*"The Lord of this city."* The source commentary explains the specification: *this city* (*balad*) is thought to refer to Makkah, though God is the Lord of all lands (al-Ṭabarī). It is mentioned specifically because it is the home of the idolaters to whom the Prophet ﷺ is preaching; it is a reminder of the blessings and benefits that accrue to them by virtue of being inhabitants of Makkah, where they are safe from attack (al-Ṭabarī).

*Balad* is from *b-l-d*, a city, a town, a land — the same root as *balad*, country, and *buldān*, the lands. The Qur'an uses the term of Makkah and of other places:

> *"And [I swear] by this city (bi-hādha'l-balad) — and thou art a resident in this city"* (Qur'an 90:1–2)

> *"Nay, I swear by this city"* — and: *"And thou art free [of blame] in this city"* (Qur'an 90:1–2)

> *"And We made the House a place of return for mankind and a security"* (Qur'an 2:125)

The specification of Makkah at verse 91 is rhetorically powerful, and the commentators develop the point in several directions.

**It is a claim of lordship over their own city.** The Quraysh's objection to the Prophet ﷺ was not to God's existence but to His exclusive lordship and to the Prophet's ﷺ authority. Verse 91's declaration — *the Lord of this city* — asserts that the God the Prophet ﷺ worships is the Lord of the very ground the objectors stand on, of the Kaʿbah they venerate, of the sanctuary they inhabit, and of the safety they enjoy. The claim is not foreign; it is local.

**It reminds them of the blessing they are living in.** Al-Ṭabarī's reading is explicit: the mention is a reminder of the blessings accruing to them as inhabitants of Makkah, where they are safe from attack. The Qur'an states the blessing at 106:3–4:

> *"So let them worship the Lord of this House (rabba hādha'l-bayt), Who has fed them against hunger and secured them from fear."* (Qur'an 106:3–4)

Verse 106:3 uses *rabba hādhi'l-bayt* — the Lord of this House — where verse 91 uses *rabba hādhihi'l-baladah* — the Lord of this city.
