The two are nearly identical, and both pair the local lordship with the two blessings of provision and security. Verse 29:67 makes the same pairing about Makkah's inhabitants:

> *"Have they not seen that We have made [Makkah] a secure sanctuary (ḥaraman āminan), while people are snatched away from all around them?"* (Qur'an 29:67)

**It appropriates their own vocabulary.** The Quraysh already called God *rabb hādha'l-bayt* — the Lord of this House. The Kaʿbah was venerated, the sanctuary was recognized, and the safety was acknowledged even by the pagan Arabs, as the source commentary notes: *its trees were not to be cut or its animals hunted, prohibitions followed even by the Arabs of the time because of their abiding recognition of the sanctity of the Kaʿbah, the House of God built by Abraham, and its surroundings.*

Verse 91's declaration therefore uses language the objectors already accepted and draws the conclusion they refused: if He is the Lord of this city, He is the Lord of everything, and the worship He commands is exclusive.

**Alladhī Ḥarramahā: "Who Has Made It Sacred"**

The relative clause identifies the Lord by His act: the One who made the city sacred. *Ḥarramahā* is Form II from *ḥ-r-m*, to make forbidden, to make sacred, to sanctify — the same root as *ḥarām*, forbidden and sacred, *ḥurmah*, sanctity, *ḥaram*, the sanctuary, and *iḥrām*, the pilgrim's state.

The source commentary explains: *made it sacred* refers to the fact that people were forbidden to commit certain acts during the pilgrimage to Makkah, or it means that it is a safe place for people seeking sanctuary or refuge. Its trees were not to be cut or its animals hunted — see also 3:97, 5:97, 29:67 (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī).

The sanctity's content is specified in the Qur'an and the tradition:

> *"And whoever honors the sacred rites of God (shaʿāʾira Llāh) — verily it is from the piety of hearts"* (Qur'an 22:32)

> *"The pilgrimage is [in] known months... And take provision, but verily the best provision is piety"* (Qur'an 2:197)

> *"God made Makkah sacred on the day He created the heavens and the earth; it is sacred by the sanctity of God until the Day of Resurrection. Its trees are not to be cut, its game is not to be hunted, its lost property is not to be taken save by one who announces it, and its green growth is not to be cut."* (al-Bukhārī 112; Muslim 1353 — from Ibn ʿAbbās, on the day of the Conquest)

> *"Verily this city — God made it sacred on the day He created the heavens and the earth..."* (al-Bukhārī 1833; Muslim 1366)

> *"Verily Makkah was made sacred by God and was not made sacred by people; it is not lawful for a man who believes in God and the Last Day to shed blood therein or to cut its trees."* (al-Bukhārī 104; Muslim 1354)

The reports establish the doctrine: the sanctity is divine, originary, and permanent — God made it sacred at the creation, it is not a human institution, and it persists to the Day of Resurrection.

Verse 91's *alladhī ḥarramahā* attributes the making-sacred to God alone, and the attribution is the verse's argumentative point: the sanctity your city enjoys is His act, and the act establishes His lordship over it.

**Wa Lahū Kullu Shayʾ: "And Unto Him Belongs All Things"**

The clause moves from the particular to the universal, and the movement is the verse's logical structure. The Lord of this city, who made it sacred, *owns everything.*

The Qur'an states the universal ownership constantly:

> *"And unto God belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is in the earth"* (Qur'an 2:284; 3:109, 129, 180; 4:126, 131, 132, 170; 16:52; 22:64; 24:42; 31:26; 42:4; 57:2, 5)

> *"And unto God belongs the inheritance of the heavens and the earth"* (Qur'an 3:180; 57:10)

> *"His is the dominion (mulk) and His is the praise, and He is over all things Able"* (Qur'an 64:1)

> *"Blessed be He in Whose Hand is the dominion"* (Qur'an 67:1)

The movement from *this city* to *all things* is the verse's rhetorical ascent, and it answers the objection implicit in the specification. If God were only the Lord of Makkah, the Quraysh might argue for the legitimacy of other lords for other places. Verse 91 forecloses the argument: the Lord of this city is the Lord of everything, and the specification of the city is a rhetorical approach rather than a limitation.

Verse 39 of this sūrah used the universal claim against the nine men of Thamūd: *"we will surely raid them and their people by night"* — and the reply at verse 51 was *"behold how was the end of their plot."* Verse 91's universal ownership is the same claim stated positively: everything belongs to the One whose sanctuary they inhabit.

**Wa Umirtu An Akūna Mina'l-Muslimīn: "And I Am Commanded to Be Among Those Who Submit"**

The second command is stated in the same passive form — *umirtu*, I have been commanded — and the repetition is deliberate. The first command was to worship the Lord; the second is to be among those who submit.

The construction *an akūna mina'l-muslimīn* — "that I be among the Muslims" — uses the partitive *min*: not that I be a Muslim in isolation but that I be *among* the Muslims, one of a group. The Qur'an uses the same construction repeatedly:

> *"And I am commanded to be among those who submit"* — cf. 6:14, 163; 10:72, 104; 39:12

> *"Say: 'Verily my prayer and my sacrifice, my living and my dying are for God, Lord of the Worlds. No partner has He; and thus I am commanded, and I am the first of the Muslims (wa anā awwalu'l-muslimīn).'"* (Qur'an 6:162–163)

> *"And if they turn away, say: 'I have warned you all alike'"* — and: *"And I have been commanded to be just between you"* (Qur'an 42:15)

> *"Say: 'O my people! Work according to your capacity; verily I am working. And you shall know'"* (Qur'an 39:39)

Verse 6:163's *wa anā awwalu'l-muslimīn* — "and I am the first of the Muslims" — is the strongest form of the declaration, and verse 91's *min al-muslimīn* is its humbler form: not the first but *among*. The humility is characteristic and the commentators note it.

The source commentary lists the parallels for *those who submit* (*muslim*; cf. 10:72): 2:128, 3:52, 3:85, 3:110, 5:3, 5:111, 22:78.

> *"And who turns away from the religion of Abraham save him who makes a fool of himself? And verily We chose him in this world, and in the Hereafter he is among the righteous (ṣāliḥīn). When his Lord said unto him: 'Submit (aslim)!' he said: 'I have submitted (aslamtu) unto the Lord of the Worlds.'"* (Qur'an 2:130–131)

> *"And Abraham enjoined it upon his sons, and Jacob [likewise]: 'O my sons! Verily God has chosen for you the religion; so die not save as submitters (muslimūn)'"* (Qur'an 2:132)

> *"They said: 'We worship thy God and the God of thy fathers, Abraham and Ishmael and Isaac — one God, and unto Him we submit (muslimūn)'"* (Qur'an 2:133)

> *"When the disciples said: 'We believe; and bear witness that we are submitters (muslimūn)'"* (Qur'an 3:52)

> *"And whoever seeks other than submission (islām) as a religion, it will not be accepted from him"* (Qur'an 3:85)

Verse 2:131's *aslim* — "submit!" — and *aslamtu* — "I have submitted" — is the primal scene of the word, and verse 91 places the Prophet ﷺ in the same position: commanded, submitting, among the submitters. The chain is Abraham → Jacob → the sons → the disciples → Muhammad ﷺ, and verse 22:78 names it:

> *"And strive for God with the striving that is His due. He chose you and placed upon you no hardship in the religion — the religion of your father Abraham. He named you Muslims (muslimīn) before"* (Qur'an 22:78)

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 91 is a declaration by the Prophet ﷺ and, in the tradition's reading, a model for every Muslim's declaration. It has three components that can be adopted.

**The local lordship.** The Prophet ﷺ named the Lord of *this city* — the place where he stood, where his opponents lived, where the sanctuary was. The application is that the worship of God begins in the place one occupies: the Lord of my city, my household, my workplace, my country. Verse 91's specification is not a limitation but a starting point, and the starting point is where the reader is.

**The recognition of the blessing.** Al-Ṭabarī's reading makes the mention of Makkah a reminder of the blessings accruing to its inhabitants — safety, provision, sanctuary. The application is the one verse 73 stated generally: the bounty is being given and most do not give thanks. The reader inhabits a place with a history, a security, a provision, and he has not made the recognition. Verse 106:3–4 supplies the practice: *let them worship the Lord of this House, Who has fed them against hunger and secured them from fear.*

**The membership rather than the distinction.** The Prophet ﷺ did not say "I am commanded to be a Muslim" but "to be *among* the Muslims" — *mina'l-muslimīn*. The construction places him within a group, in a chain that begins with Abraham and continues to the end. The application is that submission is not an individual achievement but a membership, and the person who seeks to be distinguished within it has misunderstood it. Verse 3:85's warning — whoever seeks other than submission as a religion — is the negative form of the same claim.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:92

> **And to recite the Qur'ān. So whosoever is rightly guided, he is guided only for his own soul. And [as for] whosoever is astray, say, "Verily, I am but a warner."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Commission and the Two Responses**

*Wa an atluwa'l-Qurʾān; fa-mani'htadā fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsih; wa man ḍalla fa-qul innamā anā mina'l-mundhirīn* — "And to recite the Qur'an. So whoever is guided, he is guided only for his own soul; and whoever goes astray — say: 'Verily I am but one of the warners.'"

Verse 91 stated the first command: to worship. Verse 92 states the second: to recite the Qur'an. It then draws the consequence of the recitation — guidance benefits the guided, misguidance harms the astray — and closes with the Prophet's ﷺ declaration of his office.

**Wa An Atluwa'l-Qurʾān: "And to Recite the Qur'ān"**

*"And to recite the Qur'an."* The clause continues verse 91's *umirtu* — I have been commanded — supplying a second object of the command. *Atluwa* is from *t-l-w*, to recite, to follow, to read aloud — the same root as *tilāwah*, recitation, and *tālī*, one who follows.

The Qur'an uses the verb of its own recitation constantly:

> *"Recite (utlu) what has been revealed unto thee of the Book"* (Qur'an 29:45)

> *"Recite unto them the account of Abraham"* (Qur'an 26:69)

> *"Recite what is revealed unto thee of the Book and establish prayer"* (Qur'an 29:45)

> *"And recite the Qur'an in measured tones (wa rattili'l-Qurʾāna tartīlan)"* (Qur'an 73:4)

> *"And when the Qur'an is recited, listen unto it and give heed, that haply you may be shown mercy"* (Qur'an 7:204)

Verse 29:45 is the closest parallel and adds the recitation's effect:

> *"Recite what has been revealed unto thee of the Book, and establish the prayer. Verily prayer prevents indecency and wrongdoing; and the remembrance of God is greater. And God knows what you do."* (Qur'an 29:45)

The verse ends with the same awareness-formula as verse 88 of this sūrah — *wa Llāhu yaʿlamu mā taṣnaʿūn* / *khabīrun bi-mā tafʿalūn* — and the pairing connects the recitation with the accounting.

**The Verb's Dual Sense: Following and Reciting**

*Tilāwah* carries both senses — to follow and to recite — and the tradition holds both for verse 92. To recite the Qur'an is to follow it; the recitation is not mere vocalization but adherence.

The ḥadīth literature develops the point:

> *"Recite the Qur'an, for it will come on the Day of Resurrection as an intercessor for its companions."* (Muslim 804)

> *"The best of you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it."* (al-Bukhārī 5027)

> *"The parable of the believer who recites the Qur'an is as the citron: its taste is good and its smell is good."* (al-Bukhārī 5020; Muslim 797)

> *"Whoever recites a letter of the Book of God has a good deed, and a good deed is tenfold. I do not say 'Alif-Lām-Mīm' is a letter; rather Alif is a letter, Lām is a letter, and Mīm is a letter."* (al-Tirmidhī 2910, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The last report connects the recitation to verse 89's multiplication — *whoever brings a good deed shall have better than it* — and to 6:160's tenfold. The recitation of a letter produces ten good deeds, and the mechanism is the bounty of verse 73.

**The Recitation as the Prophet's ﷺ Specific Office**

Verse 92 places the recitation alongside the worship as the content of the command, and the pairing defines the prophetic office: worship God and recite the revelation. Verse 62:2 states the same pairing as the mission's description:

> *"It is He Who sent among the unlettered a messenger from among them, reciting unto them His signs, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and the Wisdom, though before they were in manifest error."* (Qur'an 62:2)

The three functions — recitation (*yatlū ʿalayhim āyātihi*), purification (*yuzakkīhim*), and teaching (*yuʿallimuhumu'l-kitāba wa'l-ḥikmah*) — are the Prophet's ﷺ commission, and verse 92 names the first.

Verse 3:164 repeats the triad, and verse 2:129 gives it as Abraham's prayer — the same Abraham of verse 91's chain of submitters. The connection between verse 91 and verse 92 is therefore structural: the Lord of this city, whose sanctuary Abraham built, commanded His final messenger to worship and to recite.

**Fa-Mani'htadā Fa-Innamā Yahtadī Li-Nafsih: "Whoever Is Guided, He Is Guided Only for His Own Soul"**

The *fāʾ* is consecutive: the recitation produces two responses, and each has its consequence. *Ihtadā* is Form VIII from *h-d-y*, to be guided, to find the way — the same root as verse 62's *khayru'l-hādīn* and verse 63's *muhtadūn*.

The construction *fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsihī* — "he is guided only for his own soul" — uses *innamā* (restriction) with the preposition *li* (for, to the benefit of): the guidance benefits *him*, not another, and not God.

The source commentary lists the parallels: *similar verses that speak of human beings' good deeds as benefitting themselves, but as of no benefit to God include 31:12, 39:41, 41:46, 45:15.*

> *"And whoever gives thanks gives thanks only for his own soul (fa-innamā yashkuru li-nafsih); and whoever is ungrateful — verily my Lord is Free of need, Generous"* (Qur'an 27:40 — this sūrah)

> *"Whoever does righteousness, it is for his own soul; and whoever does evil, it is against it; and thy Lord is not unjust toward the servants"* (Qur'an 41:46)

> *"Whoever is rightly guided is guided only for his own soul (fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsih); and whoever goes astray, he goes astray only against it"* (Qur'an 17:15)

> *"Whoever does righteousness, it is for his own soul; and whoever does evil, it is against it. Then unto your Lord you shall be returned"* (Qur'an 45:15)

> *"So whoever is rightly guided is guided only for his own soul; and whoever goes astray — say: 'Verily I am but one of the warners'"* (Qur'an 27:92)

> *"Whoever disbelieves, upon him is [the consequence of] his disbelief; and whoever does righteousness, they prepare for themselves"* (Qur'an 30:44)

> *"And whoever is grateful — verily God is Free of need, Praiseworthy"* (Qur'an 31:12)

> *"So whoever is rightly guided is guided only for his own soul; and whoever goes astray, he goes astray only against it"* (Qur'an 39:41)

Verse 40 of this sūrah is the sūrah's own earlier statement of the identical principle in the register of gratitude, and verse 92 states it in the register of guidance. The two verses bracket the sūrah's argument: gratitude benefits the grateful (v. 40); guidance benefits the guided (v. 92). Both use *innamā ... li-nafsih*, and both are followed by a statement of God's independence — verse 40's *Ghaniyyun Karīm* and verse 92's implicit *I am but a warner*.

The principle is one of the Qur'an's most consequential: **the deeds of creatures do not benefit or harm God.**

> *"If you disbelieve — verily God is Free of need of you"* (Qur'an 39:7)

> *"O mankind! You are the needy toward God, and God is the Free of Need, the Praiseworthy"* (Qur'an 35:15)

> *"And whoever strives, strives only for his own soul; verily God is Free of need of the worlds"* (Qur'an 29:6)

Verse 29:6 states the ground: God is *ghanī* — free of need — of the worlds, and therefore the deeds of the worlds neither add to Him nor diminish Him. The consequence is that the entire economy of reward and punishment operates within creation, for the creature's benefit or harm.

The ḥadīth qudsī at Muslim 2577 states the principle in its fullest form:

> *"O My servants, if the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, were as pious as the most pious heart among you, that would not add to My dominion anything. O My servants, if the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, were as wicked as the most wicked heart among you, that would not diminish My dominion anything."* (Muslim 2577 — from Abū Dharr)

**Wa Man Ḍalla: "And Whoever Goes Astray"**

The second case is stated without an explicit consequence clause — the sentence moves directly to the command to speak. The omission is significant: verse 92 does not say "he goes astray against himself" (though 17:15 and 39:41 do); it says "whoever goes astray — *say*."

The turn from description to speech-act is the verse's rhetorical pivot. The Prophet ﷺ is not instructed to explain the consequence of misguidance; he is instructed to declare his own office.

**Fa-Qul Innamā Anā Mina'l-Mundhirīn: "Say: I Am But One of the Warners"**

*"So say: 'Verily I am but one of the warners.'"* The source commentary notes: *being but a warner is a description of the Prophet found throughout the Qur'an meaning that it is not his responsibility to compel anyone to believe* (cf. 11:12, 34:46, 35:23, 38:65, 79:45).

> *"And thou art but a warner (innamā anta mundhir), and for every people there is a guide"* (Qur'an 13:7)

> *"Say: 'Verily I am only a warner, plain (baṣhīran nadhīran)'"* — and: *"I am unto you a plain warner"* (Qur'an 38:65; 11:2; 51:50–51; 79:45)

> *"And We sent thee not save as a bearer of glad tidings and a warner"* (Qur'an 17:105; 25:56; 34:28)

> *"I am unto you a plain warner (innī lakum nadhīrun mubīn)"* (Qur'an 11:2; 51:51)

*Mundhir* is the active participle of Form IV *andhara*, to warn — the same root as *nadhīr*, warner, and *indhār*, warning. The Qur'an pairs *nadhīr* (warner) with *bashīr* (bearer of glad tidings) constantly, and verse 92's restriction to the warning role is contextually determined: the verse addresses the one who goes astray, and the appropriate function toward him is warning rather than glad tidings.

The *min* in *mina'l-mundhirīn* — "one of the warners" — is partitive, and its significance is considerable. The Prophet ﷺ does not say "I am the warner" but "I am *one of* the warners," placing himself within the succession.

> *"And there is no community but that a warner has passed among them"* (Qur'an 35:24)

> *"Say: 'I am not a novelty among the messengers (bidʿan mina'l-rusul), nor do I know what will be done with me or with you'"* (Qur'an 46:9)

Verse 46:9 is the Qur'an's most explicit statement of the Prophet's ﷺ place in the succession: not a novelty, not unprecedented, but one among the messengers. Verse 92's *mina'l-mundhirīn* is the same claim, and it connects to verse 91's *mina'l-muslimīn* — both partitive, both placing the Prophet ﷺ within a chain rather than above it.

The two partitive declarations at adjacent verses form the sūrah's final self-description: I am commanded to be *among those who submit* (91) and to say that I am *among the warners* (92). The submission and the warning are the two offices, and both are held in membership with those before.

**The Verse's Application**

Verse 92 states a principle with immediate practical consequences for the reader who calls others, and for the reader who is called.

**For the caller.** The guidance of the one guided benefits him, not you; the misguidance of the one astray harms him, not you. The caller's task is the recitation (*an atluwa'l-Qurʾān*) and the warning (*innamā anā mina'l-mundhirīn*), and neither includes producing the result. Verse 80 said the dead cannot be made to hear; verse 81 said the audience is those who believe and submit; verse 92 says the caller recites and warns and leaves the rest.

The application is a discipline of detachment from outcome. The person who teaches, advises, parents, or preaches is frequently consumed by the response of the one taught, and the consumption distorts the teaching. Verse 92 releases the teacher: recite, warn, and know that the guidance belongs to the guided and the misguidance to the astray. Verse 79's *fa-tawakkal ʿalā'Llāh* is the corresponding posture.

**For the called.** Whoever is guided is guided *for his own soul* — the benefit is his, entirely and irreversibly. Whoever goes astray is astray against himself. The verse removes the two most common distortions of religious practice: the belief that one's righteousness is a favor to God, and the belief that one's sin is an injury to God. Neither is the case. Verse 29:6 states the ground — God is free of need of the worlds — and verse 92 states the consequence.

The practical form of the principle is that religion is not a transaction in which the creature supplies something God lacks. It is a medicine the patient takes for himself, and the physician's interest is not increased by the taking. Verse 17:15's *fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsihī* and verse 39:41's parallel state the same.

**For the reader of the Qur'an.** The command to recite is given to the Prophet ﷺ and, in the tradition's reading, to every Muslim by extension. The recitation is the office of the community, and verse 92 places it before the guidance and misguidance of others — which is to say, the recitation precedes and enables the outcome.

The practical instruction is the one the tradition derived from Muslim 804: recite the Qur'an, for it comes as an intercessor. The recitation is not preparation for the guidance; it is the guidance in operation, and verse 77 said the Book is guidance and mercy for the believers.

---
## Sūrah al-Naml 27:93

> **And say, "Praise be to God! He will show you His signs, and you will know them." And thy Lord is not heedless of what you do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Verse: Praise, Promise, and Awareness**

*Wa quli'l-ḥamdu li'Llāh; sa-yurīkum āyātihī fa-taʿrifūnahā; wa mā rabbuka bi-ghāfilin ʿammā taʿmalūn* — "And say: 'Praise be to God! He will show you His signs, and you shall recognize them.' And thy Lord is not heedless of what you do."

Verse 93 is the last verse of Sūrah al-Naml, and it closes the sūrah with three movements: a praise, a promise, and an assurance. The verse is short, and its brevity is characteristic of Qur'anic conclusions — the argument has been made, and what remains is the praise, the future verification, and the awareness of God.

**Wa Quli'l-Ḥamdu Li'Llāh: "And Say: Praise Be to God"**

The verse opens with a command to speak, and the speech is *ḥamd*. *Al-ḥamd* is from *ḥ-m-d*, praise, commendation — the same root as *Muḥammad* (the praised one), *Aḥmad* (more praised), and *ḥamīd* (praiseworthy). The Qur'an's opening verse is *al-ḥamdu li'Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*, and *ḥamd* is the revelation's primary act of speech.

The distinction between *ḥamd* and *shukr* is a classical one, and it bears on verse 93's position after verse 73's diagnosis of ingratitude. *Shukr* is gratitude for a benefit received, and it is expressed in the heart, the tongue, and the limbs. *Ḥamd* is praise for excellence, whether or not a benefit has been received, and it is expressed in the tongue. Every *shukr* includes *ḥamd*, but not every *ḥamd* is *shukr*.

Verse 93 commands *ḥamd* rather than *shukr*, and the choice is appropriate to the context. The verse follows the eschatological argument of verses 82–90, in which the objectors are gathered, questioned, silenced, and cast into the Fire. The *ḥamd* is not gratitude for a benefit but praise for the justice, the wisdom, and the truth of what has been described — including the punishment.

The Qur'an's pattern of concluding arguments with *ḥamd* is consistent:

> *"And the last of their call will be: 'Praise be to God, Lord of the Worlds (al-ḥamdu li'Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn)'"* (Qur'an 10:10)

> *"And praise be to God, Lord of the Worlds"* (Qur'an 27:59; 37:182; 39:29, 75; 45:37)

> *"And it will be said: 'Praise be to God, Who has removed from us sorrow'"* (Qur'an 35:34)

> *"And the angels will be upon its edges, and there will bear the Throne of thy Lord above them, on that Day, eight"* — and: *"And they will be judged with justice, and it will be said: 'Praise be to God, Lord of the Worlds'"* (Qur'an 39:75)

Verse 27:59 is the sūrah's own refrain — *Praise be to God, and peace be upon His servants whom He has chosen* — and verse 93 returns to it at the conclusion. The sūrah's frame is therefore: praise at verse 59, praise at verse 93, with the argument between.

Verse 39:75 is the eschatological parallel, and its placement — after the judgment has been rendered and the two groups have been assigned — is identical to verse 93's placement after verses 89–90. The praise is the response of the assembly to the completed judgment.

The ḥadīth literature establishes the *ḥamd*'s position:

> *"Every matter of importance that is not begun with 'bismi'Llāh' is cut off"* — and: *"Every matter of importance that is not begun with praise of God is cut off (abtar)."* (Abū Dāwūd 4840; Ibn Mājah 1894, graded ḥasan)

> *"The best of remembrance is 'lā ilāha illa'Llāh,' and the best of supplication is 'al-ḥamdu li'Llāh.'"* (al-Tirmidhī 3383; Ibn Mājah 3800, graded ḥasan)

The second report identifies *al-ḥamdu li'Llāh* as the best of supplication — *afḍalu'l-duʿāʾ* — which is remarkable, since *ḥamd* is praise rather than request. The tradition explains: the praise is the supplication because it establishes the relation from which the request proceeds, and the person who praises has already received what the request seeks.

**Sa-Yurīkum Āyātihī: "He Will Show You His Signs"**

*"He will show you His signs."* The *sīn* of *sa-yurīkum* indicates the near future — "He will soon show you" — and *yurīkum* is Form IV of *r-ʾ-y*, to show, to make see. The object is *āyātihī* — His signs.

The source commentary gives two readings:

**The universal reading**: *He will show His signs, both within the soul and in the created order* (al-Ṭabarī), echoing 41:53:

> *"We shall show them Our signs upon the horizons and within themselves (fī'l-āfāqi wa fī anfusihim), until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth. Is it not sufficient concerning thy Lord that He is a Witness over all things?"* (Qur'an 41:53)

> *"And upon the earth are signs for those possessing certitude, and within your own souls. Do you not then see?"* (Qur'an 51:20–21)

**The eschatological reading**: others see this as a warning to the disbelievers that they will see the signs of God, but only after it is too late, referring to the signs at the end of the world (al-Rāzī).

The two readings are supported by different parts of the sūrah, and the tradition holds both.

**The universal reading** connects verse 93 to verses 86–88 — the night and day as signs, the mountains passing like clouds — and to the whole of al-Naml's sign-vocabulary (*āyāt* at vv. 1, 12, 14, 26, 46, 47, 59, 81, 82, 83, 84, 86, 90, 92, 93). The promise is that the signs already being shown will be shown more fully, both in the world (*al-āfāq*) and in the soul (*al-anfus*), until the truth becomes clear.

Verse 41:53's *ḥattā yatabayyana lahum annahu'l-ḥaqq* — "until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth" — supplies the terminus of the showing: the clarity is the endpoint, and the showing continues until the clarity arrives.

**The eschatological reading** connects verse 93 to verses 82–90 — the beast, the trumpet, the terror, the gathering, the questioning, the Fire — and to verse 6:158's principle that the signs arriving at the end do not benefit. The promise is that the objectors will see the signs they demanded at verse 71 (*when will this promise, if you are truthful?*) and will recognize them, and the recognition will be too late.

Verse 40:85 states the eschatological version of the too-late recognition:

> *"But their faith did not benefit them when they saw Our punishment — the practice of God which has passed among His servants"* (Qur'an 40:85)

> *"On the day some of the signs of thy Lord come, no soul's belief will avail it"* (Qur'an 6:158 — quoted at v. 71)

The two readings are not in conflict. The signs are being shown now, in the horizons and in the souls, and the showing continues; and the signs will be shown at the end, and the showing will be final. The person who recognizes them in the first showing benefits; the person who waits for the second does not.

**Fa-Taʿrifūnahā: "And You Shall Recognize Them"**

*"And you shall recognize them."* *Taʿrifūn* is from *ʿ-r-f*, to know, to recognize, to be acquainted with — the verb of knowledge by familiarity, distinct from *ʿalima* (to know by learning). The form *ʿarafa* denotes knowing something by its marks, recognizing what one has encountered.

The choice of verb is significant. The signs will not be *learned* as new information; they will be *recognized* as things already known. Verse 41:53's *yatabayyana* — becomes clear, distinct — is the same idea from another angle: the truth does not arrive as novelty but as clarification of what was already there.

The recognition has a specific application to al-Naml's narrative method. The sūrah has narrated Moses, David, Solomon, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, the ant, the hoopoe, and Sheba; it has argued from the creation of the heavens and earth, the alternation of night and day, the passage of the mountains, and the record of everything hidden. The reader who has followed has been accumulating recognition, and verse 93 promises that the accumulation will resolve into knowledge.

Verse 84's accusation — *did you deny My signs while you did not encompass them with knowledge?* — is answered by verse 93's promise: the signs will be shown and will be recognized, and the encompassing that was absent will arrive. Whether it arrives in time is the verse's unstated question.

**The Ḥadīth on the Signs' Recognition**

The tradition connects verse 93's promise to the recognition at the Day:

> *"The servant will be brought on the Day of Resurrection and will say: 'My Lord, did I not believe in Thee, and honor the messengers?' And God will say: 'Verily you had a covenant with Me...' and his mouth will be sealed"* — cf. 36:65

> *"No soul will die until it has seen its place in the Garden or in the Fire."* — and the reports on the recognition at the Bridge

The most relevant report is at al-Bukhārī 6573 and Muslim 183, in the long ḥadīth of the intercession:

> *"God will say: 'O Muhammad, raise thy head; speak and it shall be heard; ask and it shall be given; intercede and thy intercession shall be accepted.'"* (al-Bukhārī 4712; Muslim 193)

And the report at Muslim 2842 describing the Day:

> *"The people will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection upon a plain, and a caller will call: 'Let everyone follow what they used to worship.'"* (al-Bukhārī 4581; Muslim 1822 — the report of following the false gods)

The recognition in these reports is of what one worshipped and what one did, and verse 93's *fa-taʿrifūnahā* includes it.

**Wa Mā Rabbuka Bi-Ghāfilin ʿAmmā Taʿmalūn: "And Thy Lord Is Not Heedless of What You Do"**

The final clause of the sūrah is an assurance, and its form is emphatic. *Wa mā rabbuka bi-* — "and thy Lord is not..." — uses the strengthened negation with *bāʾ* discussed at verse 81, and *ghāfil* is from *gh-f-l*, heedlessness, negligence, unawareness — the same root as *ghaflah*, heedlessness, and *taghāful*, deliberate inattention.

*Bi-ghāfilin* — "heedless" — with the *bāʾ* of confirmation: thy Lord is *not* one who is heedless. The negation of the identity is stronger than the negation of the act: not "God does not neglect" but "God is not a neglecter."

The Qur'an uses the identical formula repeatedly, and it is one of the revelation's characteristic verse-endings:

> *"And God is not heedless of what you do (wa mā Llāhu bi-ghāfilin ʿammā taʿmalūn)"* (Qur'an 2:74, 85, 140, 144, 149; 3:99; 11:123; 14:42; 16:91)

> *"And thy Lord is not heedless of what you do"* (Qur'an 6:132; 10:61; 11:123; 27:93)

> *"Verily God is not unaware of what they do"* — and: *"And do not think God is heedless of what the wrongdoers do"* (Qur'an 14:42)

Verse 11:123 — the last verse of Hūd, widely held to be the final verse revealed — ends with the identical clause:

> *"And unto God belongs the Unseen of the heavens and the earth, and unto Him is returned the affair, all of it. So worship Him and rely upon Him; and thy Lord is not heedless of what you do."* (Qur'an 11:123)

Verse 11:123 and verse 27:93 share their final clause, and verse 11:123's preceding commands — *worship Him and rely upon Him* — are verse 91's *umirtu an aʿbuda* and verse 79's *fa-tawakkal ʿalā'Llāh*. The two sūrahs end with the same three elements: worship, reliance, and the assurance of non-heedlessness.

Verse 10:61 gives the assurance in its most complete form:

> *"And thou art not in any affair, nor recitest thou concerning it any recitation, nor do you any deed, but that We are witnesses over you while you are engaged therein. And not an atom's weight in the earth or in the heaven escapes thy Lord, nor is there anything smaller than that or greater, but that it is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 10:61)

Verse 10:61's *kitābin mubīn* is verse 75's clear Book, and its witness is verse 74's knowledge of the breasts. Al-Naml's final verse therefore does not introduce a new claim; it restates the claims the sūrah has already established — the record (75), the interiors known (74), the nothing hidden (75) — in the formula of non-heedlessness.

Verse 14:42 supplies the clause's consolatory force:

> *"And do not think God is heedless of what the wrongdoers do (wa lā taḥsabanna Llāha ghāfilan ʿammā yaʿmalu'l-ẓālimūn); He only defers them unto a Day when eyes will stare fixedly"* (Qur'an 14:42)

The verse addresses the one who is wronged and might suppose that the delay indicates inattention. The answer is that the delay is deferral, not heedlessness, and the Day is appointed. Verse 93's clause performs the same function for the reader who has followed the sūrah's account of the objectors: they were not overlooked, their case is recorded, and the showing of the signs is coming.

**ʿAmmā Taʿmalūn: The Shift to the Plural**

The clause uses the second-person plural — *taʿmalūn*, what *you* (plural) do — where the preceding clauses addressed the Prophet ﷺ in the singular (*wa qul*, *sa-yurīkum*). The shift is standard for Qur'anic verse-endings and extends the assurance from the Prophet ﷺ to the community and to every reader.

The shift also completes the sūrah's address. Al-Naml has spoken to Moses, to David and Solomon, to the ant, to the hoopoe, to Sheba's council, to the Thamūd's faction, to Lot's people, to the objectors of Makkah, to the Children of Israel, and to the Prophet ﷺ. The final clause addresses *you* — plural, comprehensive, including every reader of every age — and it states that the Lord is not heedless of what they do.

**The Verse's Function as the Sūrah's Conclusion**

Verse 93 closes al-Naml, and its three components correspond to the sūrah's three movements.

**The praise** answers the sūrah's opening. Verse 1 described the Qur'an as the verses of a clear Book; verse 2 as guidance and glad tidings; verse 3 as the description of those who establish prayer and give charity and are certain of the Hereafter. The *ḥamd* is the response to the whole: praise for the Book, the guidance, the glad tidings, and the certainty.

Verse 15's *al-ḥamdu li'Llāhi'lladhī faḍḍalanā* and verse 59's *wa quli'l-ḥamdu li'Llāh* are the sūrah's earlier instances, and the three *ḥamd*s — at verse 15, verse 59, and verse 93 — distribute the praise across the narrative, the refrain, and the conclusion.

**The promise of the signs' showing** answers the sūrah's argumentative section. Verses 59–64 asked five questions and demanded proof; verses 65–72 addressed the Unseen and the demand for a date; verse 82 described the beast and mankind's failure of certainty. Verse 93's promise is that the signs *will* be shown and *will* be recognized — which is to say, the failure of certainty is not permanent, and the showing will produce the recognition that was missing.

**The assurance of non-heedlessness** answers the sūrah's eschatological section. Verses 83–90 described the gathering, the questioning, the silence, the good deed rewarded, and the evil deed cast into the Fire. The assurance is that the Lord is not heedless of what is being done — which is to say, the accounting is real, the record exists, and nothing is being missed.

**The Verse's Application: Living at the End of Al-Naml**

Verse 93 leaves the reader with three tasks, and each is available immediately.

**Say the praise.** The command is *wa qul* — and say — and the speech is specified: *al-ḥamdu li'Llāh*. The task is not to feel praise but to say it, and the saying is the tradition's best of supplication. The person who closes his reading of the sūrah with the phrase has done what verse 93 commands, and the phrase takes two seconds.

**Expect the showing.** The signs are being shown — in the horizons and in the souls (41:53), upon the earth and within yourselves (51:20–21) — and the showing will continue until the truth becomes clear. The task is attention: to look at the night and the day (v. 86), at the mountains and their passing (v. 88), at the record and the interiors (vv. 74–75), and to recognize what is being shown. Verse 84's accusation — you denied what you did not encompass — is avoided by the attention, and the attention is what verse 66 said was missing.

**Live inside the awareness.** The final clause is the sūrah's last word to the reader, and it is the word that makes everything else operative: thy Lord is not heedless of what you do. The person who holds this will act differently in the private act, in the concealed injury, in the small thing verse 99:7 promises will be seen. Verse 75 said nothing hidden is outside the Book; verse 93 says the Lord is not heedless of what you do; and the two together are the Qur'an's final word in al-Naml — that the reader is being attended to, precisely, continuously, and without omission.

That is where the sūrah of the ants ends: with praise, with the promise that the signs will be recognized, and with the assurance that nothing has been missed. The sūrah began with the letters Ṭā-Sīn and the verses of a clear Book, and it ends with the Lord not heedless of what you do — the Book at one end and the Reader of the Book at the other, and the human being, watched and addressed, between them.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Naml]**
